# Job 1-19

## JOB

## THE ARGUMENT

*The argument of any book is like the crystal to a watch, or a pure translucent tiffany cast over rich embroideries; which do not darken what is under them, but set them off, and instead of hiding, beautify. It might be wished, that we could give you such an iconism, and such a transparency to this book; than which none (in all the holy volume) has more need of a shining lantern at the porch to enlighten both itself, and the reader; for, though most excellent and glorious things be contained in it, yet they seem to partake the same portion with their subject, being (as his prosperity was) clouded often with much darkness and obscurity: and that, not only in those things which are of less moment, and edification, (viz. the time, and place, and penman, etc.) but in points of higher doctrine, and concernment; which (whether it be from their own inborn sublimity, or the originary ambiguities and uncertainties of the hard language which presents them) we can but peep into, and conjecture at, rather than discern; being forbidden clearer apprehensions, by that thick and large veil which is spread everywhere upon them: and this has prevailed so far upon some judgments, that they have suspected all for a mere parable, and will believe Job to have been no other, than, what he says the life of man is, a fancy and shadow (Job 14. 2): but I shall desire a great prophet, and apostle, to refute this infidelity: in the mouth of two such witnesses (had we no further evidence) let the truth and honor of this Scripture be established forever: neither of whom would have proposed our hero as an example of brightest holiness, and patience, if he had been no more than a dim fiction, nor have taught us the noblest acts and duties of religion by things imaginary, and which never were (Ezek. 14. 4.; James 5. 10, 11): the book is observed indeed to be a sort of holy poem, but yet not a fable; and though we cannot expressly conclude when or by whom it was written, though our maps cannot show us what Uz was, or where situate, yet cannot this Scripture of Job be rejected, until atheism grow as desperate, as his wife was, and resolve with her to curse God, and die. For the partition, or method of the book, no invention can perform that better than the infallible Spirit of God has, who divides it (by St. James) into the patience of Job, and the event or catastrophe of it: and so makes it a diviner kind of tragicomedy; where many griefs and miseries determine sweetly in greater joy and mercy. This joy is advanced by the black shadowings of sorrow, and that sorrow is heightened by many sad and doleful aggravations; whilst not only what he is deprived of, oppresses him, but even what he enjoys, and his very comforters are as miserable as his losses. So is he assaulted on all hands, and had not stood possibly in the combat, but that he was impregnably armed with wisdom, and righteousness, and patience: his wisdom appears singular in that deep variety and intermixtures of all learning and philosophy, there being not any art, or profession, which all the schools have boasted, without a clear footstep and intimation here (Ch. 9. 5, 6, 7, 8, etc.). The second breaks forth, not more in the faithful character and testimony of God's own Spirit, than in his own humble acknowledgments, and actions: which (let his uncharitable judges censure them for hypocritical, or what they please, yet) demonstrate all along his strong sincerity, and holy purpose; especially in this, that they breathe and long after a better liberty and purity, which in themselves they had not, and disclaiming their own perfection (Ch. 9), seek it from his, who found folly in the very angels, and had given him sure persuasions, and full hopes, through the life of his Redeemer, that after a short corruption in the grave, his deflowered skin and flesh should be renewed, and those very eyes which had been eaten out by worms, should in heaven see God forever (Ch. 19. 25, 26): which belief and knowledge of Christ, though weak and dim, yet prove Job, whatever countryman he was, to have been a true Israelite, and a son of Abraham, though a Gentile, nay an Edomite: it is not the letter gives this descent, but the Spirit; nor does circumcision, or things outward make a Jew, (that is, a true member of the Church,) but the inward praises of the heart (Rom. 2. 28, 29): in these Job excelled many that call Abraham Father, having an unextinguished light of the true Messiah glimmering in his soul, which was more admirable in him, because his day-star was not yet risen; and yet is such a day-star to us, that had we no more to condemn atheism, and unbelief, and idolatry, and hypocrisy, and blindness, but this one history, we should yet be left unexcusable, there being so much in it to instruct us concerning both the Creator, and the creature; the blessed nature, attributes, providence, and service of him, and the empty mocking frailty, and unprofitableness of this. And then last, his patience, which is the most renowned grace of all; thus stands open and aloft in every chapter, and has left him the fairest monument and copy of divine fortitude, and constancy, that the Church of God has recorded. This patience is so much the more honorable, because his former condition was so large and prosperous: because his knowledge and grounds were darker, than those which later discoveries have blessed Christians with; and because his trials and concussions were more violent. He was a butt exposed to all arrows, and both heaven, and earth, and hell shot terribly at him: he was afflicted by God himself, by men, and by devils. He was not spared in any comfort that human life enjoys, but stripped utterly naked; both friend and foe were cruel to him; and a general conspiracy and fullness of misery beleaguered, and wounded him. No wonder if sometimes natural infirmity betrayed itself, and that we find some less faithful, and temerary, and excessive complaints bursting from him. In such united perturbations and stormings, who can but pity his immoderation, his staggerings: and read those easy lapses both with fear, and compassion? Notwithstanding, in the main he continued victorious, and not only worsted real difficulties, but verbal: those bitter criminations and aspersions of his friends, he refuted as easily, as those from Satan: and after a strenuous and vehement dispute with them, God himself who sat in the chair, and moderated, and was glorified by him, glorifies himself in declaring the unseen (but never unjust) end of his chastisements, and gives the triumph to his servant. To whom he restitutes a multiplied felicity, increasing both his friends and his riches, and brought him off that tragical and mournful theater with far greater glory than he entered upon it: and so has left us an example as well of his goodness, as of his power: and together has taught us both our calamity and happiness; that seeing the patience of Job, and the end of the Lord in it, we might not murmur, when visited, nor decide God's secret favor by outward pressures; but believe firmly that none shall ever lose by honoring the Lord, and that we may continue children and beloved, even when most sharply corrected, and therefore account them happy, that suffer for his sake, and that no joy is greater, than such temptations.*

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1. ***{There was a man in the land of Uz, whose name was Job: and that man was perfect and upright, and one that feared God and eschewed evil.}* Of the penman of this book, see on Ps. 87. 4. Cant. 1. 1.

In this first verse, the principal object of the ensuing history, is set forth by his nature and sex. *A man.* By his habitation, *In the land of Uz.* By his name, *Job.* By his virtues and good qualities, *perfection, uprightness, fear of God and care to avoid evil.*

*{There was a man}* The word איש is used sometimes only to distinguish the male from the female, as Gen. 2. 23. where the name of woman is given to Eve, because she was taken out of man. Sometimes it signifies not an ordinary man, but a great and famous man where it is opposed to, or distinguished from, Adam; as Ps. 49. 2. & Ps. 62. 9. where it is expressed by *men of high degree.* And such a man was Job, the greatest of all the men of the East, verse 3.

*{In the land of Uz}* See on Chap. 9. 24. upon *Earth.* Uz, a part of the country of Idumea, as Lam. 4. 21. so called from Uz, the son of Dishan, the son of Seir, Gen. 36. 28. mentioned, Jerem. 25. 20. Or, a part of Arabia, bordering upon the land of Edom, others conceive because the places from whence Job's friends came, were thereabouts. Three persons are mentioned in Scripture by the name of Uz. The first the son of Aram, son of Shem, son of Noah, Gen. 10. 23. The second was the firstborn of Nahor, Abraham's brother by his wife Milcah. Gen. 22. 21. The third, the son of Dishan, the son of Seir, Gen. 36. 28. From whom the place here mentioned seems to be called Uz, his posterity seating themselves in those parts.

*{Whose name was Job}* The Hebrew word שם signifies, 1. The proper name of a man, by which he is called. Isa. 4. 1. 2. The knowledge or taking notice of one. Mic. 4. 5. 3. Fame, or renown. Num. 16. 2. 4. The person himself. Ps. 20. 1. 5. A man's posterity. 1 Sam. 24. 21. 6. Help from one. Ps. 44. 5. Here it is taken in the first sense, for a man's name. This shows, that this book contains a true history, and not a mere parable, as some would have it. For his name is here set down, and mentioned also, James 5. 11. and joined with Noah and Daniel, Ezek. 14. 14. whose histories, without doubt, were not parabolical, but true and real. Also his country is named, and the country of his friends, and the places one near another. It seems he lived before the Israelites planting in Canaan; else he would not have offered sacrifice in his own country, but gone to Jerusalem, as was commanded. Neither would Job have offered sacrifice himself, verse 5. but have brought it to the priests, who used to offer it in the land of Canaan. And there is no mention of the deliverance out of Egypt, and wonders in the wilderness, which many other books of canonical Scripture, mention, and might upon several occasions have been mentioned here; if they had been done before this time. See on Gen. 36. 33.

*{and that man was perfect}* He was sincere, without fraud or dissimulation, Ps. 32. 2. & 18. 23. John 1. 47. A sincere heart is the root of all piety, Gen. 6. 9. & 17. 1.

*{and upright}* Just in all his dealings towards men, verse 8. Chap. 2. 3. & 8. 6. & 33. 27. upright in his ways, Chap. 4. 6. Prov. 19. 27. Ps. 15. 2, 3. as appears at large, Chap. 29. & 31. See Isa. 26. 7.

*{and one that feared God}* One that was truly religious, and made conscience of duty to God, as well as to man, 1 King. 18. 12. Nehem. 5. 15. & 7. 2. Acts 10. 2. For *God.* See on Chap. 20. 29.

*{and eschewed evil}* Evil of sin, the cause of all other evils; as Ps. 34. 14. & 37. 27. Isa. 1. 16. 1 Pet. 3. 11. Noted here as an effect of the fear of God, Prov. 8. 13. & 16. 6. All this was the more commendable in him, in that he was not carried away with the idolatry and unjust dealing of the Edomites, among whom he lived, but shined like a star among them, in piety and righteousness, than if he had so lived in Abraham's family, or among the Israelites; as it was to Noah to be upright in the old world, Gen. 7. 1. to Lot in Sodom. 2 Pet. 2. 7, 8. to Joseph in Egypt, Gen. 41. 18. Phil. 2. 15. The word רע signifies, 1. Evil of sin. Ps. 51. 4. 2. Evil or punishment. Amos 3. 6. Here is meant evil of sin.

**V. 2. ***{And there were born unto him seven sons, and three daughters}* Here is set out God's blessing on Job, in giving him so numerous a posterity.

*{seven sons}* Sons and daughters have their name in Hebrew from בנה to build, because they are built up by their parents, and build up the family. Job had more sons than daughters, and his name and family was likely to be spread so much the more, whereas daughters are translated to other men's names and families. The names of families are preserved in males, extinguished in females. Hence זָכָר, a male, from זֵכֶר, memory: נשים, women, from נשה, to forget. For *Son.* See on Chap. 14. 21.

*{three daughters}* God showed his bounty to him, in giving him issue of both sexes.

**V. 3. ***{His substance also was seven thousand sheep, and three thousand camels and five hundred yoke of oxen, and five hundred she asses, and a very great household, so that this man was the greatest of all the men of the East}* He proceeds to the description of Job's great wealth and prosperous estate, large enough to afford him comfort, and enrich his posterity.

*{His substance}* His children and riches are declared to commend his virtue in his prosperity, and his patience and constancy, when God had taken them from him. By substance is meant cattle, which were their greatest riches in those times. And so the word is translated, Exod. 12. 38. Gen. 4. 20. & 26. 14. and may be here.

*{seven thousand sheep}* He begins with his sheep, because they were most in number of all his cattle. The word signifies lesser cattle, as sheep, and goats; especially where it is opposed to greater, as Gen. 12. 16. Levit. 1. 2, 10. Such served for food and clothing. The word אלף signifies. 1. An ox, Ps. 8. 7. 2. A thousand. Gen. 20. 16. So here.

*{three thousand camels}* The Scripture testifies, that there was much use of such cattle in the Eastern countries to carry burdens, Gen. 37. 25. They were strong cattle, and had some two bunches on their backs, some, one; fit to keep their burdens settled: and one on their breasts, which they leaned on, when they couched down to receive their burdens.

*{five hundred yoke of oxen}* These were of great use for plowing of the ground, verse 14. 1 King. 19. 19. Amos 6. 12. בקר signifies. 1. The morning. Isa. 33. 2. 2. An ox. Exod. 22. 1. So here.

*{five hundred she asses}* Or, *asses;* without distinction of sex, for all his cattle are set down, and there is no distinction of sexes mentioned in his other cattle. And so the word is translated, verse 14. Gen. 45. 23. The she-asses are expressed, because these were kept most frequently for breed. Such cattle they used to ride on in those parts. Judg. 5. 10. and Judg. 10. 4. and to carry burdens also, Gen. 43. 26. & 44. 13.

*{and a very great household}* Abundance of servants employed by him. His children were reckoned before, verse 2. The word comes from עבד a servant. Or, *very much husbandry.* Abundance of fields and gardens to till and dig, Gen. 35. 14. These go together, for where there is much husbandry, there must be many servants.

*{The greatest of all the men of the East}* The richest and powerfulest, and most famous for wealth and honor, of all those that lived in those Eastern parts, Gen. 24. 35. Or, *of greatest authority*, 2 King. 4. 8. His wealth is here largely set out to show his sincere piety, that forgot not God in so great prosperity; which man's corrupt heart is of itself very prone unto, Deut. 8. 11–17. & 32. 15. 2 Chron. 26. 16. Prov. 1. 32. and to make way for the praise of his future patience in cleaving to God in the loss of so great an estate, Ps. 44. 17–22. *Men of the East,* Heb. *Sons of the East.* Gen. 25. 6. who lived eastward in respect of Canaan, Judg. 6. 3. Gen. 29. 1. 1 King. 4. 30. Such were the Arabians, Chaldeans, etc. Or, of those Eastern parts, simply considered in respect of the world, for Canaan, in likelihood, was in no great request in Job's time, who is thought to live long before the Israelites conquered Canaan. קדם signifies. 1. Ancient, Deut. 33. 15. 2. The East. Num. 23. 7. So here.

**V. 4. ***{And his sons went and feasted in their houses, every one his day, and sent and called for their three sisters to eat and to drink with them}* Way is now made to the ensuing history of the great crosses, that fell on Job by the loss of all his children.

*{his sons}* His daughters did not make feasts, nor invite their brethren, for that might have seemed an immodest act, not beseeming virgins; neither had they any conveniency to do it, not having houses of their own, but living in their father's house, as the manner of virgins was, Judg. 11. 31. 2 Sam. 13. 7.

*{went and feasted}* That is, no more but made feasts. No journey here is intended. So, *Go tell thy Lord,* 1 King. 18. 8. *Go tell that Fox,* Luke 13. 32. Or, *came together, and feasted*. For the word signifies coming, as well as going, Jon. 1. 3. This added to Job's comfort, that his children were so loving one to another, and declared their good will by mutual feasting. It was also an addition to his sorrow, when he lost them. The word *feasts,* is taken from a word that signifies *to drink;* for choice wines and drinks, were a principal part of their feasts, Gen. 43. 34. Est. 1. 8. הלך signifies. 1. Going about anything. 1 King. 18. 8. 2. Walking. Gen. 3. 8. 3. Passage of ships. Ps. 104. 26. 4. Dying, or going out of the world. Gen. 15. 15. 5. Constancy in a condition. Gen. 15. 2. 6. A course of life. Ps. 1. 1. Here it is taken in the first sense, for going about a business. *Feasted.* Heb. *Made a feast.* For *Made.* See on Chap. 14. 9. or *Bring forth.*

*{in their houses}* Every one in his own house, according to his turn. For *Houses.* See on Chap. 15. 28.

*{his day}* It may be each on his birthday, which used to be kept in those parts, Gen. 40. 20. Matth. 14. 6. and it may be Job kept his before; which soon after he curses, Chap. 3. 1. Or on such a day, as by mutual agreement, came to each one's turn to invite. For *Day,* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{sent and called for their sisters}* Or, *sent some to call their sisters.* So Matth. 14. 10. Herod sent, and beheaded John, that is, he sent one to behead him; as Mark 6. 27. See more of this phrase on verse 5. They neglected not their sisters, though they invited not them, but sent servants to invite them, and attend upon them, showing hereby the loving respect they bore to their sisters: yet no doubt there was a special hand of God's overruling providence in it, that Job might be the more fully tried, by the loss of all his children in one day. For *Sent* see on Chap. 14. 20. For *called* on Ch. 13. 22.

*{to eat and to drink with them}* To feast with them, as Eccl. 2. 24. Isa. 22. 13. Luke 22. 19. The word אכל signifies 1. To eat. Exod. 34. 28. 2. To devour anything. V. 16. of this chapter. *Consumed.* Heb. *Eaten.* Here it is taken in the first sense for eating with the mouth.

**V. 5. ***{And it was so, when the days of their feasting were gone about, that Job sent, and sanctified them, and rose up early in the morning, and offered burnt offerings according to the number of them all: For Job said, It may be, that my sons have sinned, and cursed God in their hearts: thus did Job continually}* The great care that Job had to keep his children in the favor of God, is set down by many particular circumstances in this verse; which shall be expounded in their order.

*{when the days of their feasting were gone about}* When all their turns were over, before they begin to feast again. Or, if there were more days than one of feasting in one house, Job might offer sacrifice after every several turn. This shows his great care of them, that would not defer the work any longer, than the end of their feasting.

*{Job sent and sanctified them}* He sent messengers unto them to require them in his name to prepare themselves (as verse 4. see there) in a holy manner to join with him in offering a sacrifice to God for them next day. This he does as a father, and likewise as a master of a family, and priest of God for that family. This did further set out the care that Job had of his children's spiritual good. The sanctification here spoken of, contains an expectation of a sacrifice; and readiness to attend upon it at the time appointed: as in Num. 11. 18. the people are bidden to sanctify themselves against tomorrow, that is, to expect flesh then to eat, and to be ready to receive it. Also it may contain a legal or ceremonial kind of purging and cleansing themselves by washing their clothes, abstaining from the marriage bed, and such like ceremonies. As the people of Israel are commanded to do against the receiving of the law, Exod. 19. 10, 14, 15. 1 Sam. 16. 5. John 11. 55. Or, by prayer and fasting the day before, and humbling themselves, they were to fit themselves for the sacrifice, that it might be accepted. And above all, by internal and spiritual means, as mortification, good meditations, Gen. 35. 2. repentance of their errors in their feasts, reconcilement to one another; that without uncleanness they might be partakers of the action, and benefit of their father's sacrifices offered, for them.

*{and rose up early in the morning}* This further illustrates Job's fatherly care of his children, in that he would not put it off any longer, but the next day in the morning, and that rising early as soon as light affords conveniency for such service. A thing noted in Scripture, as a sign of sedulity in any employment, 2 Chron. 36. 15. Ps. 101. 8. Jer. 25. 4. & 29. 19. Hos. 5. 15. Also the morning was the fittest time for God's service, when men were freshest, before they were engaged in their worldly businesses. Therefore that time was chosen by godly men for prayer, Ps. 5. 3. & 63. 1.

*{and offered burnt-offerings}* Of which himself might take no part, but they were all offered in fire unto God, and passed away in smoke, and were turned into ashes. See on Ps. 20. 3. & 37. 20. Offering in Hebrew, is lifting up, because the priest lifts it up on the altar to be burnt, and it mounts upwards in smoke, and the people lifted up their hearts and eyes after it, Ps. 141. 2.

*{according to the number of them all}* Either seven sacrifices for his seven sons, who made the feasts, or ten, including his three daughters also, who might forget themselves, and offend God in their mirth, as well as his sons: he offered for every one of them an offering of reconciliation, which declared his religion toward God, and care of his children. See Levit. 1. 10.

*{For Job said}* I.e. thought in his heart. For thought is the speech of the mind; as words, of the tongue, Ps. 39. 1. & 14. 1. & 32. 5. Gen. 20. 11. I thought. Heb. *said.* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{It may be that my sons have sinned}* See his godly jealousy over his children, that though he knew no hurt in their feasts, nor had heard none by information, yet considering how ready man's nature is, especially in his youth, to offend at such times, he suspects the worst, that they might be guilty of immoderate mirth, immodest gestures, prodigality, abuse of God's gifts, forgetfulness of the poor, and other sins of carnal incogitancy. For *son,* see on Chap. 14. 21. Eccles. 7. 2. Or, *thought unfitly of God*, as follows.

*{and cursed God}* In Hebrew it is, *blessed God:* as 1 King. 21. 10, 13. Job 1. 11. & 2. 5, 9. Because the crime of blasphemy at that time was esteemed so enormous and execrable, that men could not endure to hear it called by the proper name, but although they had a word to express it by, yet they so detested that sin, that they called it by the contrary. So a Sodomite and a whore, have their names in Hebrew from holiness, being both most unholy, Chap. 36. 14. Gen. 31. 50. & 38. 21, 22. Deut. 23. 18.

*{in their hearts}* Job thought his children might have some irreverent thoughts concerning God in the heat and liberty of their feasting. For had he known they had openly blasphemed God, he would never have endured it, but would have forbidden them those wicked feasts. Yet knowing evil thoughts of God to be sinful, he offers sacrifice for them. For *Heart* see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{Thus did Job continually}* Heb. *all the dayes.* Either after each feast was ended, or after the courses of feasting were all gone once about, and before they began again. The latter is most agreeable to the beginning of the verse, unless each brother feasted more than one day at one time. For *Did.* See on Chap. 14. 9. on *Bring forth.* For *Days.* See on Chap. 14. 14.

**V. 6. ***{Now there was a day, when the sons of God came to present themselves before the LORD, and Satan came also among them.}*

Here begins the history of Job's troubles, which are said to take their rise from Satan's malice.

*{There was a day}* That is, a certain time.

*{the sons of God}* The good angels, Job 38. 7. Luke 20. 36. They are God's children by creation, according to God's image, as Adam was, Luke 3. 38. They are like God in power, wisdom, and holiness.

*{came}* From doing God's will on earth, they returned to heaven. Or, *appeared before God*, for the end following. For *Came.* See on Chap. 15. 21.

*{to present themselves}* In an especial manner, or for a special end. It may be, to give an account to God of their service done on earth for the good of his people. So God examines Satan, verse 8. what he had seen in Job. Or, they came to offer him their service in some peculiar business; as 1 King. 22. 21.

*{Before the LORD}* Or, *By,* or, *near the Lord.* So the word is rendered, 2 King. 22. 19. Zech. 4. 14. Gen. 18. 8. They that stand on the right hand, or on the left, stand not before, Matth. 25. 33. Because our infirmity cannot comprehend God in his majesty, he is set forth unto us as a king sitting on his throne, that our capacity may be able to understand that which is spoken of him. And his angels are likened to great officers, that at certain times appear before kings, to give an account of their employment. See the like, 1 King. 22. 19.

*{Satan}* Heb. *The Adversary.* The article intimates a principal adversary. As, *The Son of man,* Matt. 25. 31. That is, the most excellent son of man. This title is in Scripture attributed sometimes to ungodly men, who show themselves enemies to God's people; as 1 King. 5. 4. & 11. 14. In both which places *Satan* in Hebrew is translated *adversary.* But here it is meant of the devil, who is the greatest enemy the people of God have, 1 Chr. 21. 1. and is therefore called the accuser of the brethren; Rev. 12. 10. because he not only hates them himself with an irreconcilable hatred, but also labors by accusing them to God, to make him their adversary also, Zech. 3. 1. So *the sons of God,* are sometimes men, but in this verse they are angels.

*{came also among them}* Heb. *in the midst of them.* Not into heaven; for being once cast out, he can come no more there. But it is a similitude taken from earthly courts, where advocates and accusers come all together: and it intimates unto us, that as the good angels are ready to go forth at God's command for the good of men, so is Satan ever ready to further his own malicious plots against them. Neither came he for good, as the angels did, but presents himself impudently to get a commission to hurt Job.

**V. 7. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, Whence comest thou? Then Satan answered the LORD and said, From going to and fro in the earth, and from walking up and down in it.}* Satan like a subtle adversary, and crafty accuser, lays nothing to Job's charge, till God give him occasion by his question, that so he might hide his malice, which appears to be great against Job, by his ready exception against him, without any study or delay, verses 9, 10. He had sought to undo Job by prosperity before; now he plots how to do it by adversity.

*{the LORD said to Satan}* He made his intention known to him by some spiritual way unknown to us. So angels speak one to another; and as men speak to God in their hearts, Exod. 14. 15. 1 Sam. 1. 13.

*{Whence comest thou?}* This question is asked after the manner of judges, who are to find out men's faults by inquiry. See the like, Gen. 3. 9. & 4. 9. For God knew well enough where Satan had been, and what he had been doing, and that he came with a heart full fraught with malice against Job.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{From going to and fro}* Zech. 1. 10, 11. & 6. 7. 1 Pet. 5. 8. Satan has no legs to go to and fro, yet he removes from one place to another in a spiritual manner, as angels do. He is sometimes in one place, sometimes in another: he is not everywhere like God, nor unmovable like a tree, though he cannot go as men do. Herein is described the nature of Satan, who is ever ranging for his prey, as one unquiet, seeking always to do mischief. Yet Satan did not intend so much in this phrase, for he would not accuse himself to God, but he sought to keep himself from giving account of the hurt he had done; as if he had said, I have been doing no harm, only I have walked up and down in the world, which is permitted to me; like Gehazi his excuse, *Thy servant went no whither,* 2 King. 5. 25.

*{in the earth}* For he had nothing to do any more in heaven. See the notes on verse 6. For *Earth.* See on Chap. 9. 24.

*{From walking up and down in it}* He sets a good face on it, as if the earth were the place of his delight and full liberty, which is the place of his banishment and restraint, till he receive his lasting damnation, Luke 10. 18. Rev. 12. 12. John 14. 30.

**V. 8. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, Hast thou considered my servant Job, that there is none like him in the earth; a perfect and an upright man, one that feareth God, and escheweth evil?}* Satan not yet bolting out his malice against Job, God gives him a fuller occasion by a particular inquiry after Job's integrity.

*{Hast thou considered}* Heb. *Hast thou set thy heart on?* That is, Have you thoroughly observed his whole conversation? So Hag. 1. 5. Exod. 9. 21. 1 Sam. 4. 20. & 25. 25. A fit question, for Satan uses thoroughly to sift God's people, Luke 24. 31.

*{my servant Job}* Though all creatures be God's servants, and owe him duty and obedience, Ps. 119. 91. and good men in a more special manner, Isa. 65. 13. yet are they more particularly so styled in holy writ, whom God does adorn with most eminent graces, and employ in most eminent places; as Moses, Num. 12. 7, 8. Deut. 34. 5. David, Ps. 78. 70. & 89. 20. Paul, Rom. 1. 1. John, Rev. 1. 1. yea and Christ himself, Isa. 42. 1. Matth. 12. 18. Job may be called God's servant here, because he worshiped the true God, though he lived among idolaters; or else because of his eminent graces mentioned verse 1. and here repeated.

*{like him}* That is, equal to him. For, no doubt, there were many good men alive, who were like Job, though they came short of those excellent graces that were in him, in regard of degrees. So the word is used, 1 Kings 8. 23. & 21. 25. Ps. 86. 8. Deut. 33. 26. 2 Sam. 7. 22. Exod. 15. 11. Mic. 7. 18.

*{in the earth}* Or, *in the land.* To wit, wherein he dwelt. As he was greater in wealth than the rest, verse 3. so also in piety. So the word is used, for a particular region, verse 10. Ps. 101. 8. Gen. 12. 1, 6. Jer. 5. 30. & 12. 4, 12. & 23. 10.

*{a perfect, etc.}* The following words were all expounded verse 1.

**V. 9. ***{Then Satan answered the LORD, and said, Doth Job fear God for nought?}*

*{Then Satan answered the LORD}* Satan had no wickedness to accuse Job of, else no doubt he would have done it. Yet he will accuse him of hypocrisy, although he could not possibly know his heart. See Satan's great malice.

*{Doth Job fear God, etc.}* He propounds it by way of question in a kind of admiration, as wondering that God should speak so well of Job, who might serve God for by-ends, as many other rich men did.

*{for nought}* Without an ample reward, out of pure love: or rather has he not his reward beforehand? as verse 10. He serves you not as a child, but as a mercenary servant, Chap. 14. 6. The word חנם sometimes signifies for naught, or without reward or pay, as here, and Gen. 29. 15. Exod. 21. 2, 11. Sometimes, without cause, or wrongfully, as Job 2. 3. Ps. 35. 7. 1 Sam. 19. 5. & 25. 31. Sometimes, without success, or in vain; as Ezek. 6. 10.

**V. 10. ***{Hast not thou made an hedge about him, and about his house and about all that he hath on every side? Thou hast blessed the work of his hands, and his substance is increased in the land.}*

*{Hast not thou, etc.}* Now he goes about to prove his former accusation, that Job did not serve God out of love, but only for reward. Because he knows that you use to work wonderfully for him, which he perceives in the mighty protection of him and his, and increase of his cattle, therefore he does all these righteous actions.

*{made an hedge about him}* A metaphor taken from a field, or a vineyard fenced round about with a strong hedge, that no cattle or wild beasts or thieves come in to do hurt there. See Ps. 80. 12, 13. Isa. 5. 2, 5. Hereby in Scripture is intimated God's special providence defending his people from all evil. This is their safeguard. This is sometimes set out by a wall, which is the safety of a house, Ezra 9. 9. Zech. 2. 5.

*{and about his house}* You have not only protected himself, but also his house and dwelling place, or rather his household, children, servants, his whole family. So the word is taken, Zech. 13. 1. 2 Sam. 7. 16, 25. Gen. 18. 19. Josh. 24. 15. Gen. 39. 5. Luke 19. 9. Acts 16. 34. For *House,* see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{and about all that he hath}* His cattle, and goods. Such things as he has abroad in the fields.

*{on every side}* So that no hurt, no not the least, can come to him, or his. You have not left so much as a gap for any enemy to come in to hurt him, or his, or aught he has.

*{Thou hast blessed}* Or, *And blessed.* So that the whole verse may go on by way of question. God's blessing sometimes includes all benefits spiritual or temporal; as Gen. 12. 2. & 24. 1. Ps. 1. 1. & 128. 1, 2, 4, 5. Sometimes spiritual only, as Eph. 1. 3. Sometimes temporal only, as Deut. 28. 3, 4. Gen. 1. 22, 28. & 39. 5. Deut. 7. 13. Such it here imports, as appears by the next words. And it is not likely that Satan, who would not acknowledge those graces, that God himself professed to be in Job, verse 8. would honor him so much as to take notice of them under a general term of blessing.

*{the work of his hands}* You have made all his actions to prosper, Ps. 128. 1, 2. and so have enriched him, Prov. 10. 22. For *Hands* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{and his substance}* Or, *so that his substance.* Thus this particle is translated, Chap. 7. 15. And it shows what was the fruit of God's blessing on his labors, and that not his labor, but God's benediction, gave him all that increase, 33. 5. 11.

*{substance}* Or, *cattle.* See notes on verse 3.

*{is increased}* Heb. *broken forth.* To wit, by birth, or opening the womb; as Gen. 38. 29. The word properly imports breaking out of bounds, and breaking through all things that hinder, so as it cannot be kept in. In Scripture phrase it often signifies abundant increase of living creatures; as Gen. 28. 14. & 30. 30, 43. Exod. 1. 12. So here it sets out a special blessing of God on Job, so that his cattle increased so mightily, that the fields could not hold them, nor they could not be kept within their wonted bounds, but they must break out, and seek for food abroad. It is like the bursting out of the presses with new wine, Prov. 3. 10. which argues great plenty. See Luke 12. 16–18.

*{in the land}* See notes on verse 8.

**V. 11. ***{But put forth thine hand now, and touch all that he hath, and he will curse thee to thy face.}* As if he should say; you have blessed him indeed abundantly, but you have not tried him yet. Lay some cross upon him, and if then he do not openly dishonor you, I should very much marvel.

*{But}* Heb. *And.* Yet it is often put for *But,* by way of opposition; as Prov. 14. 1–6. Or, *But surely.* Which latter word omitted in the translation, is used for corroboration, or confirmation of a thing; as Gen. 28. 19. where it should also be read, *But surely.* Satan is confident Job afflicted would curse God.

*{But put forth thine hand now}* Verse 12. God has no hands like man. It is therefore a similitude taken from man, who sometimes puts forth his hand for good to others, as Prov. 31. 20. by giving to the poor. So also God opens his hand to feed all living creatures, Ps. 104. 28. & 145. 16. Or to deliver his from their enemies, Ps. 138. 7. & 144. 7. Acts 4. 30. Sometimes for hurt: either when men put their hands out to take away men's goods, as Lam. 1. 10. or stretch them out to smite or strike others to their hurt, Est. 6. 2. 1 Sam. 26. 9. or to lay hold on them to imprison them; as 1 King. 13. 4. So God stretches out his hands to hurt men by taking away their goods, or destroying them, Isa. 5. 25. & 9. often. Ps. 106. 26. Ezek. 25. 7, 13, 16. Exod. 7. 4. So Satan would have God either to take away or destroy those precious things he had given Job, or to smite him with that hand that had been put forth to bless and protect him. For *Put forth* see on Chap. 14. 20.

*{and touch}* Sometimes the word signifies a light stroke, and shows how easy it is for God to destroy the creatures. Abimelech would not have the least hurt done to Isaac or his wife. No man must so much as touch them under pain of death, Gen. 29. 11, 29. See Ps. 104. 32. & 105. 15. Zech. 2. 8. So some think that Satan not daring to wish much hurt to one whom God so dearly loved, and not doubting but Job would easily be brought to curse God, wished that some light loss might be laid on him by God. But the contrary appears by the words following. And the word is put for a harder stroke, verse 19. by smiting; and also Chap. 19. 21.

*{all that he hath}* It seems by this translation, that the devil intended not a light loss, but much mischief to Job, that he should lose all, and be made a beggar. This is confirmed by God's large commission granted to him, verse 12. And by Satan's severe execution in the verses following, taking away all his children, and cattle, and leaving nothing but a several messenger of each loss to grieve him, and a bad wife to vex him. Others read it, *Touch any thing that he hath.* And that agrees well with what went before, you have so hedged him in, that he never felt the least loss. Lay but some light loss on him, and he will curse you to your face. Satan's intention was to prove Job a hypocrite. Now a good man might fall into impatience in the loss of all possessions. So the word כל is used for any, and that without a negative particle added, Job 8. 12. *it withereth before any other herb.* And it may be so translated. Gen. 2. 16. *Of any tree of the garden thou maist freely eat.* and Prov. 1. 17. *in the sight of any bird.*

*{and he will}* Heb. *if he do not curse thee.* Understand, then say I am a liar, or let me perish. So the Jews in earnest asseverations, leave out the imprecation to be added by others. It is a usual short term of an oath, and of a curse; and the choice of the penalty, if the party speak falsely, is left to God to choose, and to inflict. This short form of speech is often used in Scripture both by God and men; as Gen. 14. 23. Judg. 5. 8. Ps. 89. 35. & 95. 11. Isa. 5. 9. & 22. 14. & 14. 24. Jer. 15. 11. Ezek. 5. 11. & 14. 21. 1 King. 20. 23, 25. and sometimes it is more fully expressed, as 1 King. 19. 2. 1 Sam. 14. 44.

*{Curse thee}* See on verse 5.

*{to thy face}* Openly, publicly, impudently, boldly, without shame or fear, or any regard of your majesty. If you touch him; he will be angry with you, and fly in your face. So, *To the face* is used, Gal. 2. 11. and expounded verse 14. *before them all.* Satan notes the vice whereto men are commonly subject, that is, to hide their rebellion, and to be content with God's dealing in time of prosperity, but in time of adversity to curse God openly to his face, Isa. 8. 21. Rev. 16. 21. For *Face* see on Chap. 14. 20.

**V. 12. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, Behold, all that he hath is in thy power: only upon himself put not forth thine hand. So Satan went forth from the presence of the LORD.}*

*{And the LORD said unto Satan}* Here Satan's commission is set down in plain terms, and so limited, that he may not pass it to do Job any further hurt, till God give further leave.

*{Behold}* A note of admiration; as Hab. 1. 5. And indeed it might seem very strange that God at the first motion of Satan should give so much power to him over all that Job had.

*{all that he hath}* All his goods movable and immovable. All the persons belonging to him, and cattle. You have your full desire: try him thoroughly. Or, if the former words be read, *Touch anything that he has*, then God grants more than Satan asked. You think every light loss will make him curse me to my face; but you shall find him so well settled, that the loss of all will not make him to do it. We may marvel why Satan having so ample a commission, would spare Job's wife, and some of his servants. They also were in his commission; and he might have destroyed them as well as the rest, and as much as the cattle, all which he took away. Surely it was not out of love to Job, but for his own ends, that by the bad tidings of the one, or bad counsel of the other, he might be drawn to curse God.

*{is in thy power}* Heb. *in thy hand;* as Gen. 16. 6. & 39. 8. Exod. 4. 21. John 3. 35. Satan desires God to put forth his hand and smite Job in his possessions. Now lest Satan should cavil and say, that God smote him too lightly to make a thorough trial of his sincerity, God bids him to take his possessions into his hand, and dispose of them as he pleased. Yet God gave not Satan this power to gratify him, but to declare that he has no power over the creatures, further than God gives him leave to hurt them, Matth. 8. 31.

*{Only upon himself}* Hurt not him. Do nothing that may endanger his bodily health and safety. All that belongs to him are in your power, but not his person.

*{put not forth thine hand}* See notes on verse 11.

*{so Satan went forth}* He looked for nothing else, but a command to hurt Job. And as soon as he had it, he stayed not, nor answered any more, but went presently about the work to do Job a mischief. For *Went forth* see on Chap. 15. 23. on *Go out.* And on Chap. 20. 25. on *Cometh out.*

*{from the presence of the LORD}* Spoken after the manner of men, for he could go no whither out of God's presence, Ps. 139. 7, etc. The phrase signifies sometimes to go out of the places of God's special protection, as Gen. 4. 16. or from the place where God had appeared unto any, Jon. 1. 3. Here it is a metaphor taken from a servant, who having received a command from his master to do some special piece of service, leaves his master, and presently goes about it; as Zech. 6. 5, etc. Matt. 4. 22.

**V. 13. ***{And there was a day when his sons and his daughters were eating and drinking wine in their elder brother's house}* Here begins the particular enumeration of Job's calamities. Satan begins with lighter troubles, goes on by degrees, till he come to the greatest, that if light ones could not prevail, greater might. He brings them all suddenly, one in the neck of another, that Job might have no breathing time.

*{And there was a day}* It might well be the first day of the renewing of the course of their feasting, for it was in their elder brother's house, where in likelihood the course began. As the first place at table was usually given to the elder brother by way of honor, Gen. 43. 33. so very likely was the first turn in their mutual feasting.

*{when his sons and his daughters}* See on verse 4.

*{were eating and drinking wine}* Sometimes this phrase argues riot and excess, as Prov. 31. 4. Matth. 24. 37. But here it seems only to import drinking of wine for cheerfulness, which was usual in feasts, Est. 1. 7. Hence a feast is called *a banquet of wine,* Est. 5. 6. & 7. 2, 7, 8. However, it came in a time, when Job and they least suspected it, 1 Thess. 5. 3. See on 1 King. 4. 20.

*{elder brothers}* Heb. *first-born brothers.*

**V. 14. ***{And there came a messenger unto Job, and said, The oxen were plowing, and the asses feeding beside them.}*

*{And there came a messenger}* A brief kind of writing, wherein the story is not first set down, and afterwards a message sent; as 2 Sam. 18. 1, 19, 21. and elsewhere, but for brevity's sake, the thing is included in the report. The messenger seems to be one of Job's servants, that attended upon the cattle as verse 15. that was an eyewitness of the calamity, and could set it out the more passionately to trouble Job the more. And for that end did Satan spare him, when the rest were slain. The same did Satan in every calamity, that Job might quickly hear of it by one of his own servants. מלאך signifies 1. A messenger. 1 Sam. 23. 27. 2. An angel. Gen. 16. 7. Here it is taken in the first sense, for an earthly messenger. For *Came* see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{The oxen were plowing}* See on verse 3. The word חרש signifies 1. To plow, or fit the ground for seed. Chap. 4. 8. Secondly to imagine, or devise, which is a kind of plowing up the mind. Prov. 14. 22.

*{the asses}* Heb. *the shee asses.* These being taken away, all hope of breed was gone. See more on verse 3. *Feeding.* See on Chap. 42. 2.

*{beside them}* Heb. *at their hands.* Near them in place; as Neh. 3. 2, etc. 2 Chr. 21. 16. where in the original it is, *at the hand of the Ethiopians.*

**V. 15. ***{And the Sabeans fell upon them, and took them away; yea, they have slain the servants with the edge of the sword, and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.}* This is the first message of ill tidings that comes to Job's ears, and makes way for the rest. Satan begins low with oxen, and asses, but means to go as high as children.

*{The Sabeans}* Heb. *Sheba.* That is a company of robbers out of that place. It is conceived, that they dwelt in Arabia the Desert, and were a people given to pillage. Afterwards they were called *Sarac*ens, not from *Sarah,* but from *Sarac,* to rob. It is likely Job's country was near them, for these make the first booty of him. They were the posterity of Sheba, the son of Jokshan, the son of Abraham by Keturah. Gen. 25. 1, 2, 3. mentioned, Ezek. 27. 23, Isa. 60. 6. Jer. 6. 20. Ps. 72. 10. For they are said to be sent eastward, Gen. 25. 6. and so near Job, Chap. 1. 3. It is likely that Abraham's children planted themselves in the East one near another. There is another Sheba the son of Raamah of the posterity of Cham. Gen. 10. 7. But they planted themselves elsewhere, and it is not probable therefore that these Sabeans came of them.

*{fell upon them}* A metaphor from hunters, who when they have a wild beast in a gin, or shot, and laid along, fall down upon it, to lay fast hold on it. When Job's servants feared no such thing, but were quietly working with the cattle, or attending on them; verse 14. then the Sabeans came suddenly and unexpectedly upon them, and slew them. This made the cross the greater. Such an unlooked-for calamity, this word signifies, verse 16.

*{took them away}* For a prey, or a booty, from the right owners. For *Took away* see on Chap. 15. 12. on *carry away.*

*{yea they have slain}* Heb. *smitten.* The word נכה signifies variously according to the several words joined with it, so to smite with blindness is to make blind, Gen. 19. 11. To smite with the tongue is to bear false witness against a man. Jer. 18. 18. To smite a city is to destroy it. Josh. 10. 28, 30, 32, 35, 37, 39. To smite with the edge of the sword is to kill, Jer. 21. 7. Judg. 1. 8. and 18. 27. and so here.

*{the servants}* Heb. *the yong men.* Sometimes it is a name of age, but often of ministry and service; because servants for the most part are of the younger sort; so elders sometimes is put for such as go beyond others in years, but for the most part for such as are above others in office. Here, by the young men are meant the servants who attended on the husbandry and cattle, as Ruth, 2. 9, 15. נער. Signifies. 1. A child, or young man, 1 Sam. 3. 1. 2. A servant; for such ordinarily are young. Est. 2. 2. 3. A simple man, or child in understanding. Jer. 1. 6. Here it is taken in the second sense for a servant.

*{with the edge of the sword}* Heb. *the mouth:* because it devours and destroys men, as the mouth does meat, Deut. 32. 42. For *mouth.* See on Chap. 15. 5. For *Sword* on Chap. 15. 22.

*{I only}* This he adds, that Job might not comfort himself with the hope of any remaining to sustain him; therefore Job said he was naked, that is, destitute of all hopes of sustenance, verse 21.

*{am escaped alone}* Only and alone. Two words of one sense to show the greatness of the slaughter, so that it was a wonder that one could escape, and also to show that no further proof was to be expected, but his message to be credited, who only of all the servants there present was remaining.

*{to tell thee}* I suppose God would not have left me neither alive, unless it had been to bring this sad news unto you, which otherwise you could not so soon have known.

**V. 16. ***{While he was yet speaking, there came also another and said, The fire of God hath fallen from heaven, and hath burned up thy sheep and thy servants, and hath consumed them, and I only am escaped alone to tell thee.}* This is Job's second loss, the former fell upon the plowed grounds, this upon the pastures, that he might have no hope of bread or meat hereafter.

*{while he was yet speaking}* Before he had made an end of his bad tidings. The losses came so thick on Job, that there was no space left between to mourn for each loss, nor to comfort himself, or get strength by godly meditation to bear the ensuing loss, nor liberty to breathe between them.

*{the fire of God}* That is a very great fire, so great hills are called the mountains of God, Ps. 36. 6. Nineveh a city of God. Jon. 3. 3. Great wrestlings, wrestlings of God, Gen. 30. 8. Or a fire coming from heaven, as follows, and Gen. 19. 24. and so sent by God, and not kindled by men, as 1 Kings 18. 38. Num. 11. 1. So fire is put for lightning, Ps. 105. 32. 148. 8. Exod. 9. 24. and so here, this message is sadder than the former, and brought about by Satan's power and policy that Job might think God his enemy, as well as men. For *Fire,* see on Chap. 15. 34. 18. 5. For *God,* see on Chap. 20. 29.

*{Is fallen}* See on verse 15.

*{From heaven}* Out of the air. Ps. 79. 2. with Matth. 6. 26. שמים is put. 1. For the starry heaven. Gen. 1. 14. 2. For the air, Gen. 1. 20. Seldom in the Old Testament for the heaven of happiness, unless perhaps Ps. 119. 89. For that was not so clearly known then: but in the New Testament, it is called the third heaven, 2 Cor. 12. 2.

*{the sheep}* See on verse 3.

*{the servants}* See on verse 15.

*{consumed them}* Heb. *Eaten them up.* As if the fire were hungry, and had a mouth to eat, and a belly to receive the creature it destroys. See this phrase, Deut. 4. 24. and 9. 3. Isa. 33. 14. Heb. 12. 29. There might be some hope of recovering the other cattle carried away, verse 15. but these were quite gone, and burnt to ashes. For *Consumed,* see on verse 4. on *Eat.*

*{And I only, etc.}* See the notes on verse 15.

**V. 17. ***{While he was yet speaking there came also another, and said, The Chaldeans made out three bands and fell upon the Camels and have carried them away, yea, and slain the servants with the edge of the sword, and I only am escaped alone, to tell thee.}* This is Job's third loss, which deprived him of all hope of subsistence; for now all his livelihood was clean gone.

*{While he was yet speaking}* See on verse 16.

*{the Chaldeans}* A people that lived on spoil, and used not, nor knew not how to till the earth: they were called *Casdim* in Hebrew, from Chesed Nahor's son, from whom, it is likely, they descended. Gen. 22. 22.

*{three bands}* Heb. *heads:* so called, because every troop had a several head, or commander. See Judg. 7. 16, 20. and 9. 43. 1 Sam. 11. 11. & 13. 17. They had one troop to slay the servants, another to drive away the camels, a third, to oppose such as would rescue them. So that they came not accidentally, but with a purpose to rob, and soldiers set in order for that very end. For *Head,* see on Chap. 19. 9.

*{fell upon the Camels}* Like robbers to take a prey. See more on verse 15. as also on all the words following, to the end of this verse.

**V. 18. ***{While he was yet speaking, there came also another, and said, Thy sons and thy daughters, were eating and drinking wine in their eldest brother's house}* Here is Job's greatest loss set down. He might have hoped for some comfort from his children, (who lived so lovingly together) when all his cattle were gone: but now that hope fails him also.

*{While he was yet speaking}* See the phrase, Prov. 8. 26.

Look on the notes on verse 16.

*{Thy sons and thy daughters, etc.}* See on verse 13. *Brothers,* see on Chap. 19. 13.

**V. 19. ***{And behold there came a great wind from the wilderness, and smote the four corners of the house, and it fell upon the young men, and they are dead, and I only am escaped alone to tell thee}* The manner of the death of Job's children, is here set down, which might be the more troublesome, because it seemed to be done by the immediate hand of God, and men had no hand in it.

*{Behold}* A note of admiration is set before it. First, to show the great, and formidable force of the wind. For the word uses to be added, to stir up admiration of the greatest things, Gen. 6. 17. Isa. 7. 14. Secondly, to show, that as this was the last, so it was the greatest plague: for no *behold* is added to the former.

*{there came}* A metaphor from living creatures: for the wind cannot come of itself. As if senseless creatures had conspired against Job, and came of their own accord to do him a mischief. See the like of the sun, Ps. 19. 5.

*{a great wind}* The wind is powerful in itself; but there needed an extraordinary wind to do what follows. For *Wind,* see on Chap. 15. 2.

*{from the wilderness}* Heb. *from aside,* or *from beyond,* or, *from over the wildernesse.* It may be the houses of Job, and his children were not far from desert places. Yet those trees could not keep the wind off from them: or, *from behither the wildernesse;* that is, from this side of the wilderness, next Job's house. So the word is used, Neh. 3. 7. and so it should be translated, 2 Chron. 20. 2. from this side the sea, out of Syria, or besides Syria. For it could not be from beyond the sea, if they came out of Syria, or near it. The wilderness here mentioned, may be the wilderness of Edom, 2 King. 3. 8. or Arabia Deserta. Or, *from the plain,* Isa. 63. 13. Jer. 10. 10. Lam. 4. 19. and 5. 9. מדבר signifies, 1. Speech, Cant. 4. 3. 2. The wilderness, Jer. 2. 2. So here.

*{and smote}* Heb. *touched.* See notes on verse 11.

*{the four corners of the house}* A strange wind to blow so many several ways at once; this shows it to be an extraordinary wind, Dan. 7. 2. פנה signifies. 1. A corner of a house, Prov. 7. 8. 2. Chief men, or rulers, Judg. 20. 2. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{and it fell}* Matth. 7. 27. Satan does not take away Job's children, in an ordinary way by sickness, one after another; but all together, by a violent and strange death, that Job might think God was very angry with him.

*{upon the young men}* Job's sons, 2 Sam. 13. 32. including the daughters also, for they were there, verse 13. and none escaped but only the messenger. The servants likewise that attended, perished. These are called young men, verses 15, 16, 17. in the original; and Gen. 22. 5. See notes on verse 15. All the family (except the messenger) perished, but the young men only are mentioned, to grieve Job the more, that he should lose all his sons, and that in their youth: and also suddenly, and eating and drinking, and before sacrifice offered for them, as formerly, verse 5.

*{and they are dead}* If they had been only wounded, or some of them dead, the trouble had been the less, but the loss of all together was grievous, 2 Sam. 13. 30, 32, 33.

*{and I only, etc.}* See on the fifteenth verse. The Jews have an opinion, that this messenger fell down dead, as soon as ever he had delivered his message, as if he had been kept alive, only till he might tell Job these sad tidings.

**V. 20. ***{Then Job arose, and rent his mantle, and shaved his head, and fell down upon the ground, and worshipped}* Job bore the former troubles without any complaint, nay without uttering any word at all: but this last and greatest trouble makes him to express his grief in words.

*{Then}* As soon as he had heard of the loss of his sons. Either he had no leisure before to lay open his grief in words, for the sudden following of one ill message after another; or else he was more afflicted with the loss of his children, than of all the rest. The devil kept the greatest blow till last.

*{Job arose}* It may be from the table, where he was. As it was dinnertime with his children, verse 18. so it might be with him. Or rather, he left his business he was a-doing, to mourn. So the word is often used in Scripture, for leaving other business, to go seriously about one, without any relation to the former, or future posture of the body, Gen. 35. 1. 2 Sam. 14. 31. 1 Kings 21. 18. 2 Kings 1. 3. For *Arose,* see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{and rent his mantle}* Tore his upper garment, which men used to do in times of great heaviness. Some think it to be a stately robe, which men of great honor used to wear. This Job did to show, that his heart was rent within him with grief. The like did men at funerals, 2 Sam. 3. 31. Or upon any sudden news of evil to themselves, Gen. 37. 34. Or sight of great calamity fallen on their friends, Job 2. 12. 2 Sam. 1. 11. Josh. 7. 6. Or in sign of sorrow for their own sins, or the sins of others, 2 Chron. 34. 19. Joel 2. 13. Ezra 9. 3, 5. Or in detestation of blasphemy: as if their hearts were rent within them, by hearing God so dishonored, 2 Kings 18. 37. & 19. 1. Isa. 36. 22. & 37. 1. Or in sight of any other great dishonor done to God, Jer. 36. 24. Acts 14. 13, 14.

*{shaved his head}* Heb. *polled.* Either cut it himself, as well as he could, in that sad condition, or caused it to be done by some other. The hair, which of itself is an ornament to man's head, Cant. 5. 11. was wont to be cut, upon occasions of very great sorrow, Ezra 9. 3. Isa. 15. 2. & 22. 12. Jer. 7. 29. & 16. 6. & 41. 5. & 48. 37, 38. Ezek. 7. 18. Mic. 1. 16. Though at other times also, it was cut in an ordinary course for decency, or to amend prolixity procured by neglect, Gen. 41. 14. Sam. 19. 24. Of the former sort was Job's cutting his hair at this time. The word is used as well of shearing sheep, as of cutting hair, Gen. 38. 12. 1 Sam. 25. 7. 2 Sam. 13. 23. For *Head,* see on Chap. 19. 9.

*{and fell upon the ground}* Falling down in Scripture, is used as an outward gesture of worship, Deut. 9. 18. 2 Sam. 12. 16. 2 Chron. 20. 18. Matth. 2. 11. & 4. 9. Ps. 72. 11. Rev. 7. 11. Yet worship was done sometimes by standing; 1 Kings 8. 22. 2 Chron. 6. 12. By sitting, 2 Sam. 7. 18. By kneeling, Isa. 45. 23. Rom. 14. 11. Phil. 2. 10. 2 Chron. 6. 13. Ps. 95. 6. Dan. 6. 10. By bowing the head, or body, Ps. 22. 29. & 72. 9. Ps. 95. 6. Isa. 58. 5. Gen. 24. 26, 48, 52. Exod. 4. 31. & 12. 27. & 34. 8. Job here in great extremity, chooses the most submissive gesture of all the rest. For *Ground,* see on Chap. 9. 24. on *Earth.*

*{and worshipped}* The Hebrew word is sometimes used for civil worship, due to superiors, as Gen. 18. 2. & 33. 7. & 43. 26. Otherwhile, for religious worship due to God, as Gen. 24. 26, 48, 52. And so it is here taken, as appears by the verse following, where Job throws himself down before God, to give him the glory of his mercies and judgments, with reverence and humility.

**V. 21. ***{And said, Naked came I out of my mother's womb, and naked shall I return thither: the LORD gave, and the LORD hath taken away: blessed be the name of the LORD.}*

*{And said}* This verse contains Job's victory over Satan, who was so far from cursing God, as Satan confidently affirmed he would, verse 11. that he, after all his losses, gives praise and glory to God. For *Said,* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Naked came I}* Not without clothes only, but also without cattle, possessions, children, or any wealth. So the wise man expounds it, Eccl. 5. 15. and the psalmist, Ps. 49. 17. and the apostle, 1 Tim. 6. 7. by being destitute of all worldly helps, or supports. For *Come,* see on Chap. 15. 21. 20. 25.

*{out of my mother's womb}* Some expound it of the earth, the common mother, whence man was made at first, Gen. 2. 7. & 3. 19. because he mentions returning thither: and they conceive, that he delivers it under the notion of his mother's womb, by way of similitude, because the child lies hid there, as Adam's body did in the earth: and the rather, because his conception being later, was fresher in his mind, than his first parent's creation. He came immediately out of his mother's womb, but mediately out of the earth. Others, understand it of his mother's womb, out of which he was born, and give other interpretations of the words following. For *Mother,* see on Chap. 17. 14. For *Belly* on Chap. 15. 2.

*{and naked}* Or, *therefore naked.* So this particle is used, Job 21. 14. Gen. 3. 10. *And I was afraid;* that is, therefore I was afraid; to wit, because I heard your voice; Isa. 43. 4. Because I came in naked into the world, therefore must I go out of it so. And the reason consists in this: that we being born naked, our goods are not our own, but lent us for our use, while we live here, and therefore we cannot look to carry them away with us: as a servant that wears a lord's livery, must wear it no longer, when he is put out of service. Thus Job comforts himself from the common condition of all men, who must, one time or other, part with all they have. Or, it may be, he looks upon his own particular losses, as if he should have said, I thought my prosperity would have lasted till my death, but now I see all is gone before, and there is no hope of the recovery of my estate. For so Job thought, in likelihood, seeing all suddenly gone.

*{shall I return}* Our life is compared to a journey, and we to travelers. We come into the world by birth, John 18. 37. and we return by dying, Gen. 3. 19. Eccl. 12. 17. For *Return,* see on Chap. 14. 13. on *Pass.*

*{thither}* Not into his mother's womb again, for that is counted an impossibility, John 3. 4. but to the grave, the womb of the earth, his common mother, wherein his body must lie hid after death, as it did in his mother's womb before his birth: as the lower parts of the earth are put for the grave, Ps. 63. 9. so also for the womb, Ps. 139. 15. It may be he pointed to the earth, on which he lay, and then *thither,* is *to this earth.* So *this,* for *this body.* See notes on Chap. 13. 28. & 19. 26. 1 Cor. 15. 53. So *there,* Ps. 36. 12. is not referred to that which was spoken of before; but to some new thing, intervening, presented to the sense, or understanding. *There;* that is, in hell, or in the grave, where such wicked persons use to be lodged. And so *thither,* may signify here, the place whither dead men use to go; the grave, a place well known, and therefore needed not to be named. See on Eccles. 3. 17.

*{the LORD gave}* The cattle, and children which I had, Gen. 33. 5, 11. John 24. 3, 4. Job comforts himself with God's providence, looking up to him in all things, and above secondary causes. For *Gave,* see on Chap. 14. 4. on, *Bring.*

*{and the LORD hath taken away}* It is likely that Job had no knowledge of Satan's commission, but he knew that men had unjustly taken away some of his goods, yet he attributes all unto God, by whose permission he knew it was done, and that he might justly take all things from him, when he pleases, who had given him only the use of them, during his pleasure, but had kept the right in himself, as the Lord of all, Amos 3. 6. 2 Sam. 16. 10. Gen. 45. 5. 1 King. 12. 15. For *Taken away* see on Chap. 15. 12. on *carry away.*

*{blessed be}* He acknowledges that God did him no wrong, in taking away all now, (Ps. 73. 1, etc. Isa. 39. 6, 7, 8) and praises him for affording them so long unto him. God blesses us, by making us happy, who were unhappy before, and enriching us with gifts of wealth, or grace, or glory, Gen. 24. 35. & 26. 12, 13. Eph. 1. 3. Matt. 25. 34. We bless him, by praising him, and acknowledging, and making known those excellencies that are in him, Ps. 33. 2, 4. & 114. 1, 2, etc. And by giving him thanks, for all the favors we receive from him, 2 Chron. 31. 8. Dan. 2. 19. Matt. 14. 19. Ps. 103. 1, 2. etc.

*{the name of the LORD}* God himself, Ps. 44. 5. & 48. 10. and 72. 18, 19. Dan. 2. 19, 20. So names are put for persons, Acts 1. 15. Rev. 3. 4. When the name is put for the thing itself, there is an emphasis in it, signifying the excellency of the thing, and that it is most worthy to be named. So that by God's name, here is meant God himself, who is so glorious, that his boundless wisdom, and greatness deserve to be proclaimed, far and near, by men and angels. For *Name,* see on Chap. 1. 1.

**V. 22. ***{In all this Job sinned not, nor charged God foolishly}* Here God clears Job for his carriage hitherto, which proved contrary to Satan's expectation.

*{In all this}* In all these calamities and miseries that befell him, though they were very great. Yet afterwards he fails in conference with his friends; though never so far as to blaspheme God, as Satan supposed he would.

*{Job sinned not}* Not in word or deed alone, as Chap. 2. 10. but, did not yet so much as entertain a hard thought of God, or of his providence in his heart: but thought well of God's goodness, notwithstanding any evil that befell him.

*{Nor charged God foolishly}* Or, *with folly.* Heb. *nor attributed folly* or, *unsavorinesse to God.* The word signifies that which wants due or right temperature. As any kind of meat not well seasoned. Chap. 6. 6. Untempered mortar, Ezek. 13. 10, 11, 14. His heart was so well seasoned, that he had no unfitting thoughts of God and his providence, that might be unbecoming God's justice, or displeasing to him, as unsavory meat is to us. For *God.* See on Chap. 20. 29.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1. ***{Again there was a day, when the Sons of God came to present themselves before the LORD, and Satan came also among them; to present himself before the LORD.}*

*{Again}* Satan could not prevail against Job by his former commission; now God is compared to a king, that appoints a second meeting, wherein a second commission is granted to Satan.

*{there was a day, etc.}* See on Chap. 1. 6.

*{to present himself before the LORD}* This was affirmed of the good angels, Chap. 1. 6. but not of Satan till now. Some think he was summoned by God, to give an account of what he had done against Job, and to be directed for time to come. But it may be he came voluntarily with a malicious intent, to get a larger commission against Job, because he saw he could not hurt him by the former.

**V. 2. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, From whence comest thou? And Satan answered the LORD, and said From going to and fro in the earth and from walking up and down in it.}*

*{And the LORD said, etc.}* God asks the same question, and the devil had no other answer to give, than he did, Chap. 1. 7. Hereby Satan was the more confounded. See notes on Chap. 1. 7.

*{In the earth}* He had no power in heaven, where all is pure, and no liberty of temptation.

**V. 3. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, Hast thou considered my servant Job, that there is none like him in the earth, a perfect and an upright man, one that feareth God, and escheweth evil? and still he holdeth fast his integrity, although thou movedst me against him, to destroy him without cause.}*

*{And the LORD said, etc.}* God would not yet condemn Satan of wronging Job, because he intended a further trial of him, but asks him the same question he did, Chap. 1. 8. to see if he would confess his error. See notes there.

*{and still he holdeth}* Or, *and still holdeth.* So all may go on by way of question to the end of the verse. This is added, to startle Satan the more, and to put him to new resolutions. Neither could this be affirmed of Job before, because he was not tried formerly. Do you not see that notwithstanding all these grievous calamities, he holds fast his integrity?

*{fast his integrity}* Notwithstanding all these losses, whereby you did intend to get it away from him, and said he would forsake it, when all was taken away, he laid fast hold on his innocency, and would not let that go, though he parted willingly with the rest. He held fast by it, as a man in the water holds by anything that may save him, and will not let it go. Or, as a soldier holds fast his weapon or buckler, because his life lies upon it.

*{thou movedst me}* A metaphor taken from men; for God is immovable. You endeavored to stir me up by colorable reasons to destroy Job, 1 Chr. 21. 1.

*{to destroy him}* Heb. *to swallow him up.* A metaphor from wild beasts, Exod. 7. 12. Ps. 124. 3.

*{without cause}* Not for his former sinful course or wicked life, which he was not guilty of, though he have human infirmities, 1 Sam. 19. 5. Job 16. 17. etc. You have not brought any weighty accusation of any crime, for which he should be destroyed, only a malicious suspicion of your own of some ill intention hidden in him. Or, he never did you any wrong, that you should prosecute him thus eagerly to death, Ps. 7. 4. Or, *In vain,* that is, without success: you cannot prevail against him by troubling him; that appears plainly, for he is so far from blaspheming me, as you said he would, that he in all these losses, has not spoken one indiscreet word. See notes on Chap. 1. 9.

**V. 4. ***{And Satan answered the LORD and said, skin for skin, and all that he hath will a man give for his life.}*

*{And Satan answered the LORD and said}* Now Satan vilifies those things he had set so great price on before, that he made full account they would force Job to curse God. See Chap. 1. 11. As if Satan should have said, Job has lost his goods indeed, and children, but seeing himself safe, he makes no great account of their loss.

*{skin for skin}* Any skin for his own: outward things that concern not his own body are too light to try him withal. A proverbial speech signifying that Job cared not for the loss of cattle, nor servants, nor children, so he might keep his own skin whole; his own life was dearest to him. He could well be content they might all perish so he might escape; let all the rest go which way it will. He would give not only his cattle's and servants', but his sons' skins also to save his own. Or, *skin upon skin,* 2 King. 4. 5. He will give the skins of all that belong to him, rather than be hurt himself. So some understand John 1. 16. *Grace for Grace,* that is grace upon grace, abundance of grace.

*{And}* Or *Yea,* as Prov. 30. 18. for it increases the signification.

*{All that a man hath}* All his goods, garments, hangings. Such were then for the most part made of skins, Gen. 3. 21. Levit. 15. 17. He will not give something only, though very precious, but all: as if Satan should have said, I have gone to the uttermost of my commission, Chap. 1. 12. Give me a larger commission; for he will not be moved, till some evil light upon himself. See now in the notes on Chap. 1. 11, 12.

*{For his life}* Heb. *for his soul.* The cause for the effect; while the soul is in the body, there is life in it, and when it is gone the body is dead. So Gen. 9. 4, 5. & 19. 17. Or, *for himself.* That he may escape untouched. So the word is used for the person of man, Gen. 12. 5. Exod. 1. 5. and then the sense is more full, he will part with all, not only to save his life, as before, skin for skin; but he makes so light account of cattle, servants, yea, and children also, that he would rather lose them all, than expose his own person to the enduring of pain. And this is suitable to Satan's motion in the fifth verse; not to destroy Job; for then how could he accuse God; but to lay some sore disease on him. Here Satan accuses Job of unnaturalness and self-love. For *Soul,* see on Chap. 14. 22.

**V. 5. ***{But put forth thine hand now, and touch his bone and his flesh, and he will curse thee to thy face}* Satan now desires leave to make a further trial of Job, by bodily plagues laid upon him.

*{put forth thy hand now and touch}* See notes on Chap. 1. 11.

*{his bone and his flesh}* His body, which is made up of them two; Luke 24. 39. Lay pain and weakness on him, so that not his flesh only may be smitten, but his very bones also troubled, Ps. 6. 2. and 32. 3. and 51. 8. Satan does not desire, nor God give leave that he should meddle with Job's soul, to make him mad, for then there had been no trial of his carriage, when he had not been himself. The word עצם signifies. 1. Strength. Chap. 21. 23. 2. A bone, Gen. 2. 23. because the strength of the body lies in the bones, and they uphold the flesh. Here it is taken in the first sense. For *Flesh* see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{and he will curse thee to thy face}* See on Chap. 1. 11.

**V. 6. ***{And the LORD said unto Satan, behold he is in thine hand, but save his life.}*

*{And the LORD said unto Satan}* God is content to give Satan a larger commission, that Job may be further tried.

*{Behold he is in thine hand}* Before, God gave all Job had into Satan's hand, but not his own person, he might not touch him, Chap. 1. 12. Now he may. See notes on Chap. 1. 12.

*{But}* Or *Only.* A term of diminution, limiting Satan's commission, so that he might not take away Job's life.

*{Save}* Do not destroy his life. So God is said to quicken men by keeping them alive, and restoring them to health, when they are in danger of death, Ps. 119. 25, 88, 107. Afflict his body as much as you will, so you kill him not. Thus God sets bounds to evil spirits, Matt. 8. 31. Rev. 20. 2. Maimony interprets it of not troubling his mind, not depriving him of his understanding. שמר signifies. 1. To preserve, keep, or save. Chap. 10. 12. 2. To keep in prison. 1 King. 20. 39. 3. To observe, or watch, what is said, or done. Jer. 20. 10. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{His life}* Heb. *his soule.* See notes on verse 4.

**V. 7. ***{So went Satan forth from the presence of the LORD, and smote Job with sore boils, from the sole of his foot, unto his Crown}* The verse shows us, how quick and sharp Satan is, in prosecuting God's servants when he can get leave.

*{So went Satan forth from the presence of the LORD}* See notes on Chap. 1. 12.

*{smote Job}* Satan, though a spirit, yet can work upon our bodies, and inflict sores, if God give leave, yet the way how, is not set out unto us. For *Smote,* see on Chap. 1. 15.

*{With sore boils}* These sores were most vehement: with such God plagued the Egyptians, Exod. 9. 9. and threatened to punish the rebellious people, Deut. 28. 27. so that this temptation was most grievous; for if Job had measured God's displeasure by the greatness of his disease, he might have thought that God had cast him quite off. For *Sore* see on Chap. 1. 1. on *Evil.*

*{From the sole of his foot unto his Crown}* All over his body, leaving him no clear place. A proverbial speech. Isa. 1. 6. For *Sole* see on Chap. 36. 32. on *Cloud.*

**V. 8. ***{And he took him a potsherd to scrape himself withal: and he sat down among the ashes.}*

*{A potsherd to scrape himself}* Herein is set out the greatness of Job's misery. His fingers in likelihood were so sore, that he was compelled to make use of some other thing.

*{sat down}* Intending to abide there, verse 13. For *Sat down.* See on Chap. 22. 8. on *Dwelt.*

*{among the ashes}* Heb. *in the midst of the ashes,* as Chap. 1. 6. & 2. 1; 1. 17, 21. & 39. 14. Like a mourner; this posture was a sign of sorrow of old. Chap. 42. 6. Jonas 3. 6. Est. 4. 1, 3. Isa. 61. 2. Matt. 11. Luke 10. 13. Sometimes they put ashes on their heads, 2 Sam. 13. 19.

**V. 9. ***{Then said his wife unto him, dost thou still retain thine integrity? Curse God and die.}*

*{then said his wife unto him}* A temptation greater than the disease, that his wife, who ought to comfort him in his misery, should prove an adversary to him, and assist Satan against him. Satan uses the same instrument against Job, that he did against Adam, Gen. 3. 6. For *Said* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Dost thou still}* Or, *doest thou yet.* After so many sad things befallen you, notwithstanding your integrity; and, it may be, for it.

*{retain thine integrity}* What gain you by serving God, seeing he thus plagues you, as though he were your enemy? This is the most grievous temptation of the faithful, when their faith is assailed, and when Satan goes about to persuade them, that they trust in God in vain, Chap. 21. 15. Mal. 3. 14. Or, Do you continue still in maintaining your integrity: when God has discovered your hypocrisy? See Chap. 1. 8, 21. Chap. 2. 3.

*{Curse God and die}* Heb. *bless.* Bless God, though you die. Because you are so ready to praise God, even in troubles, as you did, Chap. 1. 21. go on in that way, and see how God will reward you with a painful death, which you cannot avoid. An ironical scoff at Job's piety. Or blessing is put for cursing. See notes on Chap. 1. 5, 11. Get that benefit by blaspheming God, which you cannot get by serving him, that you may die by his hands for cursing him, and so be freed from these pains. Or, Curse God once before you die, that you may be revenged on him for dealing thus unkindly with you. For *God,* see on Chap. 20. 29.

**V. 10. ***{But he said to her, thou speakest as one of the foolish women speaketh: What? Shall we receive good at the hand of God, and shall we not receive evil? In all this did not Job sin with his lips.}*

*{But he said to her}* He holds not his peace, although he were sorely afflicted with his disease, but reproves his wife for her wicked counsel.

*{Thou speakest as one of the foolish women}* As a woman that wants understanding, and makes herself ridiculous to all men, 2 Sam. 13. 13. The Hebrew word comes from a verb, that signifies the falling of a withered leaf from a tree, Ps. 1. 3. As it has no moisture in it, so there was no wisdom in her speech. Or, *as a wicked woman*: for so the word *foolish,* often signifies, Ps. 14. 1, 2. Prov. 1. 32. & 7. 22. & 9. 13.

*{What?}* Or, *Also.* Showing, that he does not only reprove her, but also gives a reason of his reproof.

*{Shall we receive good, etc.}* That is, if we be content to receive good from God, we must be content to receive evil also. So Mal. 1. 4. *They shall build, etc.* that is, if they build, I will throw down. See the like phrase, Ps. 119. 23. Is it fit we should serve God hitherto in our great prosperity, and fly from him, now he afflicts us? This fully answers Satan's two charges, Chap. 1. 11. & 2. 5. and his wife's objection, verse 9. of this chapter; and shows the sincerity of Job's heart, that he served God out of true love. By *good* and *evil,* is meant prosperity, and adversity, as Isa. 1. 19. Amos 3. 6. For *Good,* see on Chap. 21. 13. on *Wealth.*

*{In all this did not Job sin}* Notwithstanding the force of his disease, and temptation of his wife. See notes on Chap. 1. 22.

*{With his lips}* In his speeches, as afterward, he did in the following chapters. It may be he now began to fret inwardly, which he did not, Chap. 1. 22. yet broke not out into impatient words for the present. He so bridled his affections, that his tongue through impatience did not murmur against God, Ps. 39. 1. James 3. 2. For *Lips* see on Chap. 15. 6.
