**V. 11. ***{Now when Job's three friends heard of all this evil that was come on him; they came every one from his own place, Eliphaz the Temanite, and Bildad the Shuhite, and Zophar the Naamathite: for they had made an appointment together to come to mourn with him, and to comfort him.}*

*{Now when}* A new temptation of Job by his friends, who came to comfort him, and almost drove him into despair.

*{Job's three friends}* It seems these were the chief and choice of Job's friends, spoken of by number, as if he had no more. The word *friends,* in Hebrew is derived from רעה, *to feed.* For friends often eat together, Ps. 41. 9.

*{heard of all this evil}* Of all Job's losses. See on verse 10. Great and famous men's troubles are most noted and talked of. For *Heard* see on Chap. 13. 17.

*{that was come on him}* Actions of living creatures are often by way of metaphor attributed to lifeless things. So troubles are said to come on men, and to find them, and to compass them about, and get hold on them, Chap. 31. 29. Ps. 116. 3. Jon. 2. 3, 5. See notes on Chap. 1. 19. For *Come* see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{they came}* Without sending for, as friends use to do in time of adversity.

*{every one from his own place}* Out of the region where he dwelt; for they dwelt not far off in Idumea (a place famous for wise men, Obad. verse 8. Jer. 49. 7. such as these friends of Job's were) or else in some bordering part of Arabia, which was not far from Teman, Isa. 21. 13, 14. Or, *from his own place,* may be from his own house; for they might dwell nigh one another, in the same region. Else hardly would there have been so strict friendship between Job and them, had they lived far asunder, Gen. 21. 22, 23. & 26. 26, etc. Neither could they so soon, or easily have visited Job in his affliction. So *place* is put for habitation, or abiding, Chap. 6. 7. & 7. 10. & 8. 8.

*{Eliphaz the Temanite}* One of the posterity of Teman the son of Eliphaz, the son of Esau, Gen. 36. 10, 11. Or, an inhabitant of Teman, which some think to be in Arabia, out of Isa. 21. 13, 14. Others, in Edom, out of Jer. 49. 7. Amos 1. 12. Obad. verses 8, 9. However, it was a place of choice wise men. It had his name from the south, in the original, because southward from Judea, as some conceive, Gen. 36. 34. There is mention of Timnath, where Samson's father-in-law dwelt, Judg. 14. 1. and of Timnath Serah, the place of Joshua's burial. But none of these could be Eliphaz his habitation: they were too far off from Job's.

*{Bildad the Shuhite}* Of the posterity of Shuah, Abraham's son by Keturah, Gen. 25. 1, 2. 1 Chr. 1. 32.

*{Zophar the Naamathite}* Of Timnah, one of Esau's posterity, as some think Gen. 36. 40. Others think him to be of Naamah, a town in the tribe of Judah, mentioned Josh. 15. 41. But neither time nor place suit well with this story. Both are too far distant.

*{they had made an appointment together}* Or, *they were gathered together.*

*{to come}* See notes on this verse before.

*{to mourn with him}* Nah. 3. 7. Isa. 51. 19. Or, *to have compassion on him,* as Ps. 69. 20. נוד signifies. 1. To be shaken. 1 King. 14. 15. 2. To bemoan one, or mourn with him. Jer. 48. 7. So here.

*{and to comfort him}* By showing themselves sensible of his sorrows, which is a great comfort to one in misery, Rom. 12. 15.

**V. 12. ***{And when they lift up their eyes afar off, and knew him not, they lifted up their voice and wept and they rent every one his mantle, and sprinkled dust upon their heads toward heaven.}*

*{And when}* Here is set out the cause of the astonishment of Job's friends.

*{they lift up their eyes afar off}* Spying a man afar off, sitting in a sad condition, they fixed their eyes upon him, and having some suspicion it might be Job, because they had heard of his trouble, they viewed him the more earnestly to see whether it were he or no, and whether it were as bad with him, as they were informed. For *God* see on Chap. 20. 29.

*{and knew him not}* His boils had so much altered his countenance, they could not know him at first sight. Lam. 4. 7, 8.

*{They lifted up their voice and wept}* When they came nearer, and saw it was he indeed, and how much he was altered, they set up a loud lamentation for him, that even passengers might take notice of the depth of their sorrow, Gen. 50. 10, 11. 2 Sam. 18. 33. Est. 4. 1. Judg. 4. 2. This was the first gesture and sign of sorrow in them, Gen. 21. 16. & 37. 35. Ruth 1. 14. For *Seize* see Chap. 15. 12. on *Carry away.*

*{and they rent every one his mantle}* This is the second mourning gesture, Gen. 37. 34. Josh. 7. 6. Est. 4. 1. Gen. 44. 13. Judg. 11. 35. See on Chap. 1. 20.

*{and sprinkled dust upon their heads}* The third expression of their great sorrow, Josh. 7. 6. Lam. 2. 10. Ezek. 27. 30. Nehem. 9. 1. For *Dust*, see on Chap. 14. 8. For *Head*, see on Chap. 19. 9.

*{towards heaven}* Throwing it up in the air, so as it might fall on their heads. See on Chap. 1. 16.

**V. 13. ***{So they sat down with him upon the ground seven days, and seven nights, and none spake a word unto him: for they saw that his grief was very great.}*

*{So they sat down with him upon the ground}* Here are two other outward expressions of their sorrow. The one is sitting on the ground by him, Esay 47. 1. Lam. 2. 10. Neh. 1. 4. They sat with him to show that they bore part of his sorrow with him.

*{and none spake a word unto him}* The last note of their sorrow is silence. They were so grieved for him, that they could not speak a word to him, Lam. 3. 28. & 2. 10.

*{for they saw that his grief was very great}* None of them would speak to him for fear of troubling him. Sick persons love quietness. They came prepared to speak, very likely; but were astonished at his misery, finding it far greater than they expected, and stayed till Job should offer matter of discourse, that they might know, what was fit to say to him, Exod. 6. 9. For *Saw*, see on Chap. 19. 27.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1. ***{After this Job opened his mouth, and cursed his day.}*

*{After this}* Having sat so many days still, and no words passing from his friends, his choler breaks out into impatience. A strange change. He that in the first chapter blessed God after his losses, and in the second schooled his wife for ill speeches, now speaks ill himself. Yet he curses not God, and so proves Satan a liar, and his wife's counsel foolish. For he had so foretold twice, Chap. 1. 11. & 2. 5. and she had exhorted him to it, Chap. 2. 9. He curses his birthday, like one lanced by the surgeon, who dares not curse him, but curses the knife.

*{Job opened his mouth}* A man may open his mouth, though he speak not. But opening the mouth in Scripture phrase, is put for speaking; as Psal. 38. 13. *I was as a dumb man that openeth not his mouth*; that is, that speaks not: for a dumb man can open his mouth, but not speak. It is cleared by the beginning of the verse, *As a deaf man that heareth not.* So Psal. 39. 9. Prov. 8. 6. And Psal. 78. 2. To open the mouth in a parable, is to utter parabolical speeches. Opening the mouth then, is beginning to speak. It may be these friends of Job knowing him to be wise, looked upon him in silence, observing when he would move his lips, and begin to speak. For *Mouth*, see on Chap. 15. 5.

*{and cursed}* Spoke lightly of it in contemptuous words, according to the original of the word in Hebrew, Lev. 20. 9. 2 Sam. 16. 5. So Hagar despised her mistress, Gen. 16. 4. and Michal David, 2 Sam. 6. 16. See 1 Sam. 2. 30. We speak ill of, and to, things vile, and mean persons. But Job did more than that. He knew well, that the day was neither capable of blessing, nor cursing; yet out of the abundance of his grief, and pain, he curses it, and wishes ill to it, as appears in the verses following.

*{his day}* The day of his birth, vers. 3. Hos. 7. 5. Jer. 20. 14. See on Chap. 1. 4. or, This sad day, on which all these heavy things befell him, Jer. 17. 17. Obad. vers. 12. For *Day*, see on Chap. 14. 14.

**V. 2. ***{And Job spake and said}* A preparation for Job's future discourse, to move expectation, and win attention.

*{Job spake}* Heb. *answered.* Yet the word is often put for beginning to speak, Judg. 18. 14. Deut. 27. 14. Matth. 22. 1. The Jews conceive, that his friends having so long sat by him in silence, and hearing nothing come from him that might give them a hint what to say to him, asked him in few words, what he ailed. To which short question, though not recorded, Job gave this following answer. So they conceive of that in Deut. 26. 5. *Thou shalt speak.* Heb. *answer*; that the priest asked the man, what he had in his basket, and thereupon he should answer, in the words ensuing. For *Spake*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{and said}* The doubling of the word may signify Job's earnestness in speaking, not the weight of the matter worthy to be often repeated, as some would have it. It was rather worthy to be all buried; for it was very sinful. For *Said*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 3. ***{Let the day perish, wherein I was born, and the night, in which it was said, There is a man child conceived}* A hyperbolical expression of his great infelicity, in his apprehension; yet not without much impatience: as if he had said, I am the unhappiest man alive. Let there be no yearly remembrance of my birth, as of great men's, for it repents me that ever I was born.

*{Let the day}* It may be Job was born in the day; or by the day may be meant, the time simply. It is called the birthday still, though many be born in the night.

*{perish}* Or, *be lost.* Heb. *it shall perish.* The Hebrews have no optative, but use the future for it; as Cant. 1. 2. Psal. 5. 11. *Let him kisse: Let them rejoyce.* Heb. *He shall kisse: They shall rejoyce.* Or, *O that the day had perished:* as Chap. 16. 21. I would it had never been, if it were possible, for then had I never felt this misery. Or, let it be put out of the almanac; nor be numbered among the days of the year, vers. 6. Let it be as clean forgotten, as if there never had been such a day. For that is said to perish, which is out of all remembrance. So the former days of the Nazirite, after he had come near the dead were lost, Num. 6. 12. they were not to be numbered. He was to begin his days again, Chap. 10. 18, 19. Jer. 20. 14. Eccl. 4. 3.

*{wherein I was born}* See on Chap. 1. 4. Or, *in which I was to be born.* For it is the future in Hebrew.

*{and the night}* Or, *or the night.* See *and* for *or*, Exod. 21. 15, 16, 17. & 22. 22. So he would be sure to curse the time he was born in, were it day or night.

*{in which it was said}* Heb. *he said.* To wit, the messenger, who brought tidings hereof to my father, to make him glad. So Gen. 48. 1. *He said to Joseph*, that is, One told Joseph; Or, *in which he commanded, that a man-childe should be conceived*, to wit; God commanded. So much the word imports, Gen. 1. 3. Exod. 23. 13. Psal. 33. 9. and so it is translated, Esth. 1. 10. & 9. 25. 1 Chron. 21. 17. Or, impersonally, *it was said.* So Gen. 15. 6. *He counted it to him for righteousnesse*, is by the Apostle, Rom. 4. 3. rendered, *It was counted to him.* Or, *the night which said.* As if the night should utter things done in it, and being a witness of the time of Job's conception, should utter it. A metaphorical speech, taken from reasonable creatures, and applied to unreasonable. See the like, Psal. 19. 1, 2.

*{a man child}* Heb. *a man.* A name taken from strength, and prevalency, in the original. The news of men-children is more welcome to parents, or friends, than of daughters, 1 Sam. 4. 20. Gen. 30. 23, 24. Jer. 20. 15. Not only because the male is the principal sex, but also because the father's name is preserved in it. See notes on Chap. 1. 2. He meant himself; In which I was conceived. As no doubt, Chap. 7. 1. and 14. 1. speaking of man's miseries, he had an especial eye to his own.

*{conceived}* Or, *born.* Or, *brought forth.* For the time of his conception could not be known, nor any report brought, whether a male or female were conceived. So the word is used, 1 Chron. 4. 17.

**V. 4. ***{Let that day be darkness, let not God regard it from above, neither let the light shine upon it}* An execration and amplification of an unhappy day. Let it be swallowed up in eternal darkness, as unworthy to have, or give light, that no such unhappy day might be in the world.

*{Let that day be darkness}* Let it be darkness itself; that is, most dark. Let not the sun give it any light at all; or let it be clean taken out of the world. Either he wishes it had been so at first, or may be so in the yearly revolution of it. It would be a great astonishment to men, to look for a morning, and find it night still, like the three days' darkness in Egypt, Exod. 10. 23. Or, the sun darkened in his rising, Isai. 13. 10. A sad thing, when after darkness light is expected, that darkness should continue; as it were comfortable for the sun to continue at evening, when men look for darkness, Job 11. 17. Zech. 14. 7. Matth. 6. 23. Thus he expounds what he meant by perishing, ver. 3. that it should lose all light, and become night.

*{Let not God regard it}* To make it a cheerful or happy day, but let it be counted an unhappy one. This agrees with the following words. So God is said to regard, or take care for the land of Israel, Deut. 11. 12. Or, *Let not God require it.* Let him not make it return any more, but let it utterly be lost. This sense agrees with ver. 3. and ver. 6. For *God*, see on Chap. 20. 29.

*{from above}* From heaven, where he is said to dwell, or let him not make the sun shine upon it out of the sky.

*{neither let the light shine upon it}* To distinguish it from the night. See notes on this verse before.

**V. 5. ***{Let darkness and the shadow of death stain it, Let a cloud dwell upon it, Let the blackness of the day terrify it}* Out of abundance of passion, he reiterates the former wish, yet not without some new amplifications.

*{darkness}* See notes on ver. 4.

*{the shadow of death}* Or, *a deadly shade.* An augmentation adding weight to the former word. The most sad, incurable, deplorable darkness, such as seizes on the eyes of dying men, whose eyes death is ready to close. Or, such as is on dead men in the grave, where no light comes at all. Therefore God's servants, in danger of death, pray him to lighten their eyes, Psal. 13. 3. Or, the greatest darkness, such as may be felt, Exod. 10. 21. The most perilous darkness, which puts a man in fear of, and brings him into danger of death, and might kill one with the horribleness of it. Where secret snares are laid for him in the dark, that cannot be seen, or prevented. Where whatsoever men see, seems to present death unto them, or to be an image of death. Let that day be full of deadly shadows, dangerous darkness, fearful sights. For the phrase, see Job 16. 16. & 38. 17. Psal. 23. 4. & 44. 19. Isai. 9. 2. Matth. 4. 16.

*{stain it}* Or, *defile it:* or, *pollute it.* Dark hours in a day, stain the light of it, as spots in a well-colored garment. So the word is used, Isai. 63. 3. Dan. 1. 8. Mal. 1. 7. Or, *challenge it:* as a thing redeemed, which is in the redeemer's power. So Ruth 4. 4. *If thou wilt challenge it, as a thing belonging to thee, redeem it.* Or, *be kinsman to it:* Abide always with it, as if they were of one family.

*{a cloud}* A dark cloud, hindering the sun's light, Exod. 19. 9. Clouds and darkness are joined, Psal. 18. 11. & 97. 2.

*{dwell upon it}* Abide long, or be much upon it. Not pass away, as clouds are carried up and down with the winds. Dwelling, is abiding long in a place. So the word is translated, Prov. 7. 11. *He* wishes that it might be cloudy all day long, and not see one glimpse of the sun.

*{Let the blackness of the day terrify it}* Let black vapors, or thick and dark ones, drawn out of the water and earth, and going up into the air, and darkening the day, make it terrible to men. Or, make it so afraid, that it fly away. A metaphor, Chap. 18. 11. Or, *Let as it were the bitternesses of the day terrifie it.* So the word is used, Deut. 32. 24. Let it bring punishments on men, which may make it a bitter day to them. Or, *Let them terrifie it, as those who have a bitter day.* Or, *Let the priests of the day terrifie it.* Let those who used to observe and distinguish days, note it for a terrible day.

**V. 6. ***{As for that night, let darkness seize upon it, let it not be joined unto the days of the year, let it not come into the number of the months}* After the division of the day and the night, mentioned, ver. 3. in the two former verses he had spent his spleen on the day, and now he begins to curse the night.

*{As for that night}* Of his conception or birth, see on ver. 3.

*{let darkness seize upon it}* Perpetual, and unusual darkness; so that men may take special notice of it. No sign of moon or stars, Act. 27. 20. See ver. 9. and notes on ver. 4. For *Seize*, see on Chap. 15. 12. on *Carry away.*

*{let it not be joined unto the days of the year}* The natural days consisting of twenty-four hours, whereof the night is a part, Gen. 1. 5. But the artificial day is opposed to the night, Psal. 74. 16. Joh. 11. 9, 10. & 9. 4. Or, *Let it not rejoyce it selfe among the days of the year.* So the word signifies, Exod. 18. 9. Let it not be glad, that it is accounted among the days of the year. A metaphor.

*{let it not come into the number of the months}* Heb. *of the moons.* For the months were reckoned according to the motion of the moon. See notes on Rev. 11. 3. & 13. 5. He wishes this night had never been, or might never return, but be taken out of the catalogue of the nights, Psal. 69. 28. See notes on ver. 3.

**V. 7. ***{Lo, Let that night be solitary, and no joyful voice come therein}* Job goes on, in spending his passion upon the blameless night.

*{Lo}* Because he wishes new, and greater evils to it, he adds, Lo, as a note of admiration. See on Chap. 1. 12, 19.

*{Let that night be solitary}* Or, *desolate*, Isai. 49. 21. So that men meet not in it for business, or mirth; as follows.

*{and no joyful voice come therein}* Let no man make merry in it. Let there be no banquets, nor songs in it. Feasts were wont to be made in the night, and therefore are called suppers, Luk. 14. 16, 24. Rev. 19. 9, 17. and marriage feasts especially. Therefore were lamps used, Matth. 25. 3, 4, 6. and the light of a candle, joined with the voice of the bridegroom and the bride, Rev. 18. 23. See notes on Matth. 25. 1. and on Revel. 18. 23. For *Come*, see on Chap. 15. 21.

**V. 8. ***{Let them curse it, that curse the day, who are ready to raise up their mourning.}*

*{Let them curse it}* He shows the bitterness of his passion in that, he not only curses the innocent night himself, but would have others to do it also; as angry persons would have others to be angry also with those with whom themselves are offended.

*{That curse the day}* That through bitterness of spirit curse their own birthday, or the day of their trouble. See on ver. 1. They hate the light, because they live miserably; so the Ethiopians used to curse the sun-rising, because the heat of it is so extreme, that it burns both them and their country. For *Day*, see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{who are ready to raise up their mourning}* Who are as ready to weep, as if they had tears at command. Or, who have so many troubles, that when they have done mourning one hour, they are ready to begin again the next, and so by manifold complaints and lamentations renew their grief. He conceives it an easy and usual thing for those that are overwhelmed with grief, to be overruled with passion, to fret and to curse. Or an allusion to such as were hired to mourn at funerals. We read of mourning women, Jer. 9. 17. 2 Chr. 35. 25. They were hired to be leaders of the mourning. There were also men-mourners, Amos 5. 16. It may be these used in their lamentations to curse the birthday, or death-day of those for whom they mourned; and therefore are summoned by Job to curse his night. Others read it, *who are ready to raise up Leviathan.* That huge fish mentioned, Chap. 41. 1. Psal. 74. 14. & 104. 26. Isa. 27. 1. Such as can raise sea-monsters by enchantments. Or mariners, who in a storm ready to be cast away, when the whales rise up, and are ready to devour them, curse the day that ever they came into the ship.

**V. 9. ***{Let the stars of the twilight thereof be dark; let it look for light, but have none: nor let it see the dawning of the day}* Let it have no light in the evening, night or morning: let it always be night, and no day follow it.

*{The stars of the twilight thereof}* Let the evening star, and such others as use to shine before it, be quite dark, which being great stars, the light remaining in the conclusion of the day, cannot hinder the light of them.

*{Let it look for light, but have none}* Let the night look for light: for moonlight, or starlight; but let none appear. See on ver. 6. Or, *Let him look for light.* Let him that is afflicted look for some light; but not find any. Or, *Let light be looked for, and let there be none.* Impersonally. See notes on ver. 3. For *Light*, see on Chap. 17. 12.

*{Nor let it see the dawning of the day}* Heb. *The eye lids of the morning*, Job 41. 18. The eyelids in Hebrew have their name from moving, because they frequently move. The morning is compared to a man that opens his eyelids, having been shut all night. By the eyelids, is meant the opening of them; as Prov. 6. 25. *Let not her take thee with her eye-lids.* That is, with her eyes, when the lids are open; for no man would be in love with the eyelids shut. The sense is, Let it never be day more. Let not the night ever see the morning open her eyes. Let there be no light foregoing the sun-rising; nor no sunbeams to foretell the approaching of the sun. This is the greatest evil that Job wishes to the night, ver. 9. So extreme are men in their passions, that they know not where to end, till all end. For *See*, look on Chap. 19. 27.

**V. 10. ***{Because it shut not up the doors of my mother's womb, nor hid sorrow from mine eyes}* Now he shows the cause, why he cursed the day or night of his birth.

*{Because it}* The night had no power to shut his mother's womb, nor hide sorrow from him. But he speaks like a discontented man to the night, as if it had power; not daring to curse God that had it. See notes on ver. 1. Or, *Because he.* To wit, God who has power to open or shut the womb. So Gen. 50. 26. *they embalmed him*; that is, they that had the skill, and used to do it.

*{Shut not up the doors}* So that my mother could not have conceived me. So all the wombs in Abimelech's house were shut up, Gen. 20. 17, 18. And Leah's womb opened when she conceived, Gen. 29. 31, 32. and Rachel's, Gen. 30. 22. It seems not to be meant of shutting in the womb, and denying passage to life by birth; for that is mentioned, ver. 11.

*{of my mother's womb}* Heb. *of my belly.* That is, of the belly wherein I lay.

*{nor hid sorrow from mine eyes}* Kept me not from seeing pain. So seeing good or evil, in Scripture phrase, is experimental possessing of it, Psal. 34. 12. & 4. 6. Mat. 5. 8. Isa. 65. 16. Psal. 118. 5, 6. Job 33. 17. Or, *That it might have hidden sorrow*, &c. So this particle is used, Chap. 5. 11. Or, *Then it had hidden*, &c. as Chap. 13. 22. & 10. 14. I had not then lived to have seen these sad times. For *Eye*, see on Chap. 2. 12.

**V. 11. ***{why died I not from the womb? why did I not give up the ghost, when I came out of the belly?}* His passion was such, that he wishes he had died any way, no matter how, nor where.

*{From the womb}* As soon as I had life in the womb. So Psal. 110. 3. *From the womb of the morning*; that is, as soon as it begins to be light, Chap. 10. 18. For dying when he came out of the womb follows in this verse; *Why did I not give up the ghost*, &c. Heb. *Why did I not come out of the belly, and give up the ghost?* To wit, presently, or in the same moment of time, in which I was born. Suppose I had been born, why did I not presently die? For the phrase, Gen. 25. 17. Luk. 23. 46. For *Come out*, see on Chap. 15. 12. & 20. 25.

**V. 12. ***{Why did the knees prevent me? or why the breasts that I should suck?}* Because he had mentioned the birth, he mentions those things also, that by God's providence follow it; as a midwife to receive the child, and milk in the breasts to feed it, & turns God's blessings into curses.

*{Why did the knees prevent me?}* Gen. 50. 3. Why was there a midwife ready to receive me on her knees, when I was born? Why was I not suffered to fall on the ground, and lie there neglected till I died? Ezek. 16. 4, 5, 6.

*{Or why the breasts that I should suck?}* If the midwife would save my life, yet why had not my mother dry breasts, that I might not have sucked? and then I should not have lived to have felt these miseries, Hos. 9. 14, 16.

**V. 13. ***{For now should I have lain still, and been quiet, I should have slept, then had I been at rest}* He shows the cause of the former expostulation; namely, desire of escaping his troubles.

*{Have lain still}* In the grave as in a bed, Isa. 57. 2. I should have had no cause nor power to have made these sad complaints. This is the first act in bed, to lie still, and compose us to sleep. So the word signifies, Psal. 3. 5. & 4. 8. For it is distinguished from sleeping; and Deut. 6. 7. Teaching of our children is then required, which we cannot do when we sleep.

*{And been quiet}* When we are laid in our bed, we are free from the noise and business of the day. So are we in the grave free from worldly troubles, and bodily pains, Josh. 11. 23. Judg. 5. 31. Jer. 46. 27. Ezek. 16. 41. Zech. 1. 11.

*{I should have slept}* While men toss to and fro in their beds, or hear a noise, they cannot sleep. Afterwards they do. So should Job in the grave have been as free from pain, as one that sleeps soundly. Death is often called sleep in Scripture, Psal. 13. 3. Joh. 11. 11, 13. 1 Cor. 11. 30. and 15. 51. 1 Thess. 4. 13, 14.

*{Been at rest}* Nothing should have troubled me. I should have had no fearful dreams, but lain easily.

**V. 14. ***{With Kings and Counsellors of the earth, which build desolate places for themselves}* I should have been in as good a state, as kings that now are dead, and purposed while they lived to get themselves a great name by famous works, as building palaces in desolate places, and great cities.

*{with Kings}* The great and mighty monarchs of the world have no privilege over the meanest in the grave.

*{Counsellors}* Men famous for wisdom, who sit at the stern to guide kingdoms.

*{of the earth}* Not of one nation alone, but of all the world. All are in the like condition after death. Or, *of the land.* That is, of this land, so much noted among us for wisdom. See notes on chap. 1. 8.

*{which built desolate places for themselves}* Who when they were alive did so abound in wealth that they easily could and did build up decayed places, and build sumptuous palaces out of ruins, and bring abundance of people and trading into forsaken places: or, who did build cities in desert places not formerly inhabited to get them an immortal name: for there were few cities then, and such as built them were counted princes and great men, Gen. 10. 10, 11. & 11. 3, 4. or, which built stately palaces and cities for themselves and theirs to dwell in. Gen. 4. 17. Isa. 23. 13. & 58. 12. Job 15. 28. So that they and theirs dwelt alone in those places: Job 15. 19. Or, who built stately monuments and sepulchers while they lived, to be buried in when they died. To this end they bought places of burial, Gen. 23. 4, 15. & 25. 9, 10. These were ordinarily in solitary places, remote from the living. Abraham's in a field: And Elisha's out of the city, as it seems, 2 Kings 13. 20, 21. So was Christ's sepulcher in a garden, Matth. 27. 60. Joh. 20. 15. and many of the saints were buried out of the city, Matth. 27. 53. The Egyptians built curious monuments for sepulchers, as appears by the pyramids, yet remaining, which are conceived to be built by great kings for that end. For *Build*, see on Chap. 20. 19.

**V. 15. ***{Or with Princes that had gold, who filled their houses with silver.}* I should have been equal to the richest, as well as to the greatest and wisest.

*{Or}* Rather *and.* For here is no division: For he should have lain quietly with them all. So Prov. 30. 31.

*{That have gold}* That are wealthy. Eccles. 2. 8. Deut. 17. 17. *Who filled.* The Heb. word מלא signifies 1. To fill. Gen. 1. 28. 2. To fulfill, Gen. 29. 21. For *House*, see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{With silver.}* With silver household stuff and vessels, as rich men use to do. Or adorn their sepulchers richly. See on v. 14. The grave is called the dead man's house, chap. 17. 13. & 30. 23. Eccles. 12. 5. He says not with gold, for few have golden vessels; Or, who had store of silver, enough to fill their houses. An hyperbole, or excessive speech to set out abundance, as John 21. 25. For *Silver*, see on Chap. 22. 25.

**V. 16. ***{Or as an hidden untimely birth, I had not been; as Infants, which never saw light.}*

*{Or,}* This verse depends upon the former. If I had not been conceived, v. 10. or had died in the womb, or as soon as I came out of it, v. 11. or perished for want of a midwife, or a nurse, v. 12. Then I should have lain quietly with the dead, v. 13. Or, with such as were not born alive: in this verse, others read it thus, *Or, why as an hidden untimely birth, had I not been?* And so repeat *why* out of the 11. and 12. verses.

*{as an hidden untimely birth}* That lay hid in the womb, and was never seen of men, as never having life in it at all, or being stifled before it was born. The word *untimely* comes from a word, that signifies *to fall*, in the original. Falling from the mother as fruit from a tree, before it be ripe.

*{I had not been}* Not been alive, or, not lived here among men. So the word is used, chap. 7. 8. Gen. 42. 13. with 44. 20. Psal. 39. 13. Jer. 31. 15. Matth. 2. 18.

*{as Infants}* The word in Heb. is taken from a word signifying to defile, chap. 16. 15. for children in the womb are compassed about with pollution.

*{never saw light}* Psal. 58. 8. Eccl. 6. 5. were not born alive, but were stifled in the birth: we call them stillborn. For none see the light as soon as they are born; some few only cannot, who are born blind. So should Job have seen no light in the grave, Psal. 49. 19.

**V. 17. ***{There the wicked cease from troubling: and there the weary be at rest.}*

Now he goes on to show the commodities of the grave, even to those that have seen the light, and have endured many troubles in the world.

*{There}* In the grave, where dead men lie. Understood in v. 13. So, thither. chap. 1. 21. See notes there. Or, in the state of death, for some find no grave.

*{the wicked}* Oppressors. Restless, and unquiet people. It comes from a word in the Heb. that signifies motion and unquietness. Like the sea, chap. 34. 29. Isa. 57. 20. They are always studying mischief. Psal. 36. 4.

*{cease from troubling,}* Give over their rage and anger, which makes them scare, vex, and trouble other men. Eccl. 4. 1, 2. So the word signifies. Psal. 2. 1. Isa. 14. 16. and 13. 13. Job 9. 6. There Job should not have needed to fear the Chaldeans nor Sabeans, who robbed him, chap. 1.

*{the weary be at rest}* Heb. *the wearied in strength.* Who had lost their strength by the oppression of others, and the hard labors they had put upon them. Exod. 1. 14. and 2. 23. Now they may lie quietly by their oppressors.

**V. 18. ***{There the prisoners rest together, they hear not the voice of the oppressor.}*

He proceeds to show the liberty death gives to prisoners, and hard laborers.

*{the prisoners}* Debtors, bondmen, and slaves, who are cast in prison, and bound in chains.

*{rest}* Such as were prisoners on earth, rest from those labors, they were forced to undergo here. It seems their prisons were like our houses of correction, where men are forced to labor, and scourged if they do it not, Judg. 16. 21.

*{Together,}* or, *alike.* As well they who are most oppressed, as they who are less vexed. So the word is used, Deut. 12. 22. and 15. 22. 1 Sam. 30. 24. For *Together*, see on Chap. 24. 29.

*{they hear not}* As they were wont to do daily to their grief. For *Hear*, see on Chap. 13. 17.

*{The voice}* The huffy and bitter word. For *Voice*, see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{Of the oppressor}* Heb. *the exactor.* That forced men to slavish works, and required an account of them, and punished them, for their neglect. Such overseers or taskmasters the Israelites had in Egypt, Exod. 3. 7. and 5. 10. Job 39. 7.

**V. 19. ***{The small and great are there, and the Servant is from his Master.}*

Here he intimates the difference in men's political and domestical estates to be taken away by death.

*{The small and great}* Rich and poor, high and low, Jer. 5. 4, 5. Psal. 49. 1, 2, 3. See on Job 1. 3. Persons of middle rank are here also included; where both extremes are mentioned, and that according to the Scripture phrase. So; down sitting and uprising, includes things done between also, the day or night time, Psal. 139. 2. So both going out and coming in include things done abroad and at home between both, Psal. 121. 8.

*{are there}* or, *are the same there.* No difference between them. So the word is used, Psal. 102. 27.

*{The Servant is free from his Master}* There are no masters nor servants there.

**V. 20. ***{Wherefore is light given to him that is in misery, and life to the bitter in soul?}*

*{Wherefore}* He begins a new expostulation, and goes on by way of question, as v. 11.

*{Light}* The light of this world, Psal. 56. 13. why does he live, that is in misery? as in the words following. See notes on v. 16. What get men in my misery by the light? Eccl. 1. 3.

*{given}* or *why doth it give light?* The birthday, v. 3. which be cursed with so many and bitter curses. Or, *He:* That is, God, who gives all men light and life. See notes on v. 10. Yet he names not God, because he dares not complain of him, though he were offended at his dealing with him. So God is not expressed. Job 16. 7. Hab. 2. 1. For *Given*, see on Chap. 14. 4. on *Bring.* For *Light*, see on Chap. 17. 12.

*{In misery}* Toiled with labor, or pain.

*{Life}* Continuance of life to enjoy the light still, which yet allays not his pain. Or why was he not killed in the birth?

*{The bitter in soul}* Men subject to many and great miseries, which make them inwardly sad, and as it were displeasing to their souls, as bitter things are to the palate. Chap. 21. 25. it imports very great grief, Prov. 31. 6. 1 Sam. 1. 10, 15. 2 Kings 4. 27. For *Soul*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

**V. 21. ***{which long for death, but it cometh not, and dig for it more than for hid treasures.}* This is an aggravation of his misery. And it is esteemed the greatest cross to men in pain, that they cannot meet with death, which they conceive will end all their troubles.

*{which long for death}* Longing is expecting a thing earnestly, as in longing women, who miscarry if they have not what they long for. Isa. 30. 18. It is called waiting. As the word wait for a booty; Hos. 6. 9. Thence in the Heb. comes a word, that signifies the jaws, which take in meat greedily. And another which signifies the hook, which the fishes greedily devour to get the bait. Though death be bitter, yet it is gilded over with hope of ease to such as are in trouble.

*{But it cometh not}* It comes not when they would have it, though longed and much wished for. Death that seeks men in their hiding places, flies from them that seek it, Jon. 4. 3, 8, 9. Rev. 9. 6.

*{Dig for it}* Not only have strong desires of it, but also spare no labor to attain it, Gen. 26. 21, 22. As men dig pits to catch birds, or beasts, Psal. 35. 7. Or, *search for it*, Deut. 13. 12. For *Dig*, see on Chap. 39. 21.

*{more then for hid treasures}* Hidden in the bowels of the earth in mines, or by men in time of wars. Or, *out of hidden places.* They would dig deep for it, if it were to be found: they would take any pains to die.

**V. 22. ***{Which rejoice exceedingly, and are glad when they can find the grave}* This shows their great desire of death, in that they are so exceeding glad, as if they had found a thing long and exceedingly looked for.

*{rejoice exceedingly}* Heb. *to exultation*, Hos. 9. 1. They cannot keep in their joy, it is so great, but they express it by outward signs of triumph.

*{can find the grave}* When they see any likelihood of death approaching, and resting quietly in the grave. For dead men cannot rejoice, when they are carried to burial. He proceeds in the metaphor of diggers, vers. 21. who joy when they come to the golden mine. So do these, when they draw near to death. For *Finde*, see on Chap. 17. 10.

**V. 23. ***{Why is light given to a man whose way is hid, and whom God hath hedged in?}* He proceeds to set out the great misery of man in affliction, which cannot find death to ease them.

*{Why is light given}* See on vers. 20. These words may be omitted, and the words following immediately joined to the former; *Are glad, when they can finde a grave for a man whose way is hid;* That is, every one of them rejoices, when his end draws nigh.

*{whose way is hid.}* So that he can see no way of escaping out of his miseries.

*{whom God hath hedged in}* Has compassed round about with evils, so that he cannot get out. Before Job was hedged in with good things, so that no evil could come nigh him, Chap. 1. 10. But now with evils, out of which he could make no escape. Before he was hedged in, as in a garden: now, as in a prison. How great a misery is it for a man overwhelmed with sorrows, to be compelled to live, and have all passages of escaping shut up? So that the shutting up of the way, signifies inextricable calamities, out of which a man cannot expedite himself, Chap. 12. 14. & 19. 7, 8. & 38. 8. Lam. 3. 7, 9. Hos. 2. 6. Or, *whose way God hath covered.* And this answers well to the former metaphor of *hiding.* So the word signifies, Exod. 40. 21. It shows the reason, why the way of escaping was hid from Job, because God had covered it.

**V. 24. ***{For my sighing cometh, before I eat, and my roarings are poured out like the waters.}*

*{For}* Now he gives a reason of all his former curses and ill wishes.

*{my sighing cometh, before I eat}* Because of these evils I eat unwillingly, and with a sad heart, being not willing to prolong such a troublesome life with eating and drinking. Or, I do not complain without a cause, for my troubles come fresh into my mind, when I go to eat, so that I can take no comfort in meat as others do, but am so afflicted with grief, that I sigh and weep before I eat. I have no time free from sorrow, Job. 4. 34. Chap. 7. 19. Heb. *Before my bread.* See on Chap. 15. 23.

*{my roarings}* I not only sigh, but roar out for pain; when I go to eat, which other men do with comfort, and much joy, Psal. 22. 1. & 32. 3. Psal. 38. 8. A word used for lions roaring, to show the greatness of the noise his pain drew from him.

*{are poured out}* Melt into tears, and flow out of mine eyes by weeping.

*{like the waters}* In great abundance, more like rivers running out of fountains, than tears that come dropping out of our eyes, Psal. 22. 14. & 119. 136. Esai. 11. 9. Psal. 88. 17. Chap. 27. 20. Amos 5. 24. There was no intermission between roaring and roaring, as there is no distance between the drops of water. For *Waters* see on Chap. 14. 9.

**V. 25. ***{For the thing which I greatly feared, is come upon me; and that which I was afraid of, is come unto me}* I was all my lifetime afraid of these evils, even in my greatest prosperity, and now they have overtaken me.

*{For}* This shows the reason why he took no comfort in his meat, namely, the greatness of his troubles. Or, *Surely.* So this particle is translated, Chap. 8. 6. & 20. 20. as if he should have said; Surely I was not so much afraid without a cause; the event proves it: It is fallen on me just as I feared. So men in misery bewail their sorrows, by repeating the foregoing signs of it.

*{the thing which I greatly feared, is come upon me}* Heb. *I feared a fear, and it came upon me.* Fear is put for the thing feared, Prov. 1. 26. Gen. 31. 53. So love, for the thing loved, Cant. 2. 7. & 4. 10. And hope, for the thing hoped for, Col. 1. 5. My trouble is the greater, because that which of all other things I was most afraid of, and would most willingly have avoided, has notwithstanding all my preventing care, fallen upon me.

*{and that which I feared, &c.}* These words are a repetition of the words foregoing, to show the strength of Job's former fear, and present passion.

**V. 26. ***{I was not in safety, neither had I rest, neither was I quiet; yet trouble came}* He explains more fully what he meant by his fear in the former verse, to wit, a restless expectation of those troubles, that now were on him.

*{I was not in safety, &c.}* In my prosperity my mind was troubled still with expectation of these evils.

*{yet trouble came}* Or, *when trouble came.* So this particle is interpreted, ver. 22. Chap. 7. 13. I was not rid of that fear, till the very hour that this evil, which I so feared, came upon me. For *Come*, see on Chap. 15. 21.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1. ***{Then Eliphaz the Temanite answered and said}* This and the following chapter, contain Eliphaz his answer to Job's impatient discourse, Chap. 3. Eliphaz answers Job thrice, Bildad as oft, Zophar but twice. The scope of Eliphaz in this answer is to maintain that God proportions corrections according to men's sins, and therefore Job being so grievously punished, must needs be a wicked man.

*{Then}* Heb. *And.* It is used for *Then*, Dan. 8. 13. & 10. 5. When Job had showed forth his passion, and done speaking, then Eliphaz answered.

*{Eliphaz}* Not the son of Esau mentioned Gen. 36. 4. for he bears the name of Temanite, from a nephew of his, (as descending from him) who is called Teman. It may be Eliphaz spoke first because he was the eldest. So we find Elihu forbearing till they had all done speaking, because he was the youngest, Chap. 32. 4, 6.

*{the Temanite}* Descending of the foresaid Teman. Or, born in the city Teman, Jer. 49. 7. Amos 1. 12. A chief city in Idumea, famous for strong and learned men, Obad. vers. 8, 9. See on Chap. 2. 11.

*{answered}* To answer, in Scripture phrase, signifies sometimes to speak, according to the exigency of the time or hearer, though no man spoke before, 1 Sam. 9. 17. *The Lord said to him.* Heb. *answered*, Matth. 11. 25. Luke 14. 3. So did Eliphaz take occasion from Job's trouble, to speak what he thought fit to be spoken in such a case. For *Answered, and said*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{If we assay to commune with thee, wilt thou be grieved? But who can withhold himself from speaking?}* As if he had said, All this while we have stood silent, and given you leave to speak all that was in your heart; now give us leave to speak, and be not angry, for we must needs speak, and cannot forbear any longer.

*{If}* The Hebrew word ה, ordinarily is interrogative, and intimates a question; but here it is conditional, and signifies *If*; as also Chap. 5. 1. See more on Chap. 14. 14.

*{we}* I and my friends. For they were to speak as well as he. So he concludes his speech in the plural number, Chap. 5. 27.

*{assay}* The word signifies tempting to good or evil, whereby a man is assayed or tried how good or evil he is: And therefore the word in a secondary signification, is used for assaying. We will but try, if we can do you good, we will not be tedious.

*{to commune with thee}* Heb. *a word to thee.*

*{wilt thou be grieved?}* We cannot speak to you in this estate, so as to avoid all occasions of grieving you, and may displease you, rather than amend you. Your mind is so distracted and impatient, that you cannot bear good counsel, but will be extremely wearied, and faint, or rage, falling into such a grievous passion, as you were in even now; (chap. 3.) which if you do, we must discharge our consciences. Eliphaz shows that he speaks as a friend, not as a flatterer; and yet he doubts Job's mind is not so well composed as to hear him.

*{But}* See on Chap. 1. 11.

*{who can withhold, &c.}* Who can hear such impatient speeches, and be silent? Your complaints have been so bitter, and your speech so sharp, that I cannot withhold myself from speaking what I think needful in such a case, Jer. 20. 9. Luke 19. 40. The word signifies shutting up a thing, so as it cannot come out, 1 Kings 8. 35.

**V. 3. ***{Behold thou hast instructed many, and thou hast strengthened the weak hands.}*

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12. A word as of asseveration, so also requiring due consideration. It is certain all that live about you know, that you have formerly upheld others in their miseries by your good counsel, but now you sink under trouble yourself. An excellent physician you are, that can heal others, and perish yourself. You show either that your former profession was counterfeit, or that you are very impatient. Others take it ironically, like Gen. 3. 22. where the word *Behold*, is used by way of derision. It appears now, that you have not strengthened others in their afflictions, although you had a great name for such actions, either raised by yourself or others. If you had done those good things, you had not fallen into these troubles and complaints.

*{instructed}* Taught them to be patient in afflictions, and why are you now impatient yourself?

*{many}* Others also besides your own family.

*{thou hast strengthened}* You were wont to encourage men to bear their troubles patiently, and to go on in the ways of piety.

*{the weak hands}* Remiss, that hang down for want of strength to uphold themselves; as in sickness of a famine. Much less are able to work or do any business, Heb. 12. 12. Not weak out of fear, as Zech. 8. 9, 13. Isa. 35. 3, 4. Jer. 38. 4. Dan. 5. 6. But out of weight of afflictions and sorrows, Ezek. 7. 17. A metaphor from men weak in body, whereby is meant such as are dejected with sorrow for troubles lying heavy upon them. Such as by many trials are weakened, you have spoken words of comfort to, like sinews to their hands, and strength to their joints. For *Hand*, see on Chap. 15. 23.

**V. 4. ***{Thy words have upholden him that was falling and thou hast strengthened the feeble knees}* He goes on in describing Job's former good actions. To what end, or in what sense, see on vers. 3.

*{Thy words}* He shows how he did good to others in misery, to wit, by directory and consolatory words.

*{have upholden}* Or, *lifted up.* Your good counsel has kept many from falling, confirmed them, made them to stand strong like a pillar, so that they neither bowed down, nor leaned to one side or other in their miseries, but stood bolt upright, though ready to sink in regard of the greatness of their troubles.

*{him that was falling}* Ready to fall or perish. Some afflictions do so weaken men, that they are ready to sink under them, and not make them faint only, or unactive, as in the former verse. The word signifies to stumble, and so threatens a fall by reason of weakness in the joints, Isa. 31. 3. Jer. 50. 32. Falling into sin is not here intended, Gal. 6. 1. nor falling into trouble simply, as Prov. 24. 16. but a readiness to sink under trouble, and to despair.

*{thou hast strengthened}* You have made them strong to prevail against all troubles, and not to yield to them.

*{feeble knees}* Bowing ones; when men are so feeble they cannot stand upright. The knees bow, when men are ready to fall, and can stand no longer, Isa. 35. 3. Heb. 12. 12. Dan. 5. 6. Psal. 109. 24. Feeble knees argue fear sometimes, but here they are signs of weakness. The word *knees*, in the original, comes from a word, which signifies to bless, because we bow the knees in prayer, Gen. 41. 43.

**V. 5. ***{But now it is come upon thee, and thou faintest, it toucheth thee, and thou art troubled}* He seeks to convince Job either of hypocrisy in his former prosperity, or of great alteration of his carriage in his present adversity.

*{But now}* Or, *Now when it is come on thee, thou faintest.*

*{it is come}* The evil you feared, Chap. 3. 25, 26. where you did confess as much. Or, Such an afflicted estate, as others were under, whom you did comfort before, vers. 3, 4. For the word, *Come*, see on Job 1. 19. And on Chap. 15. 21.

*{thou faintest}* Gen. 47. 13. As if not only strength of body were gone, but reason also. You speak you know not what. The word is translated *grieved*, vers. 2. *mad*, Prov. 26. 18. He attributes the sense of Job's calamities expressed in his former lamentation, Chap. 3. to impatience.

*{it toucheth thee}* See on Chap. 1. 11. You are smitten with it, Psal. 144. 5.

*{thou art troubled}* Vehemently amazed, 1 Sam. 28. 21. Psal. 83. 15. Exod. 15. 15. Or, *Thou makest haste*, 2 Chron. 35. 21. for men afraid at some sudden noise or tumult, make haste away, Lev. 26. 6. Psal. 114. 3, 5.

**V. 6. ***{Is not this thy fear, thy confidence, the uprightness of thy ways, and thy hope?}* Now at length the trouble you bear, like a touchstone, has made manifest, that all those shows of virtues which you had, were counterfeits: or, if they were true, it is a wonder they appear not now there is most need. He objects to Job the want of four principal virtues, piety towards God, constancy in trouble, upright life, and hope of a good issue out of trouble. All which should be in good men. Thus Eliphaz confirms Satan's charge, Chap. 1. 9, 10. and is Satan's minister and instrument, though unwittingly, as Peter was, Matth. 16. 22, 23.

*{Is not this}* Your religion or confidence, &c. call it what you will, is come to this.

*{thy fear}* Your fear of God, your religion, your piety. See Chap. 1. 1. For *Fear* see on chap. 15. 4.

*{thy confidence}* Your boldness which you have showed in former times. A virtue which like an anchor keeps a man close to God, Prov. 3. 26. and unmovable in expectation of deliverance from evil, or good hoped for, Isa. 30. 15. The same word is translated, *Folly*, Psal. 85. 8.

*{the uprightness of thy ways}* Your just dealing with men, which you have been so much commended for, Chap. 1. 1.

*{thy hope}* Job 17. 15. Hope is an earnest waiting for deliverance from evil, or some promised reward, Psal. 37. 9. Isa. 8. 17. Rom. 8. 19.

**V. 7. ***{Remember, I pray thee, who ever perished being innocent? or where were the righteous cut off?}* Now Eliphaz falls upon the work, to prove, that no good men were ever thus punished as Job was, and therefore such great troubles befall wicked men only, and for their wickedness. He calls Job to bear witness to it, out of his own observation, taking it for granted to be so clear a truth, that it needed no proof, but would be acknowledged by Job himself.

*{Remember}* Give one instance, if you can, of any good man, perishing by the hand of God, as you are like to do. I can give instance of many bad men, that have been so destroyed, ver. 8. Search the records of antiquity, for an example of your sorrows.

*{I pray thee}* He handles Job gently now in words to win him to his opinion: and to that end he uses a preface, fit to procure goodwill and attention. The word נא signifies 1. Now, Cant. 3. 2. And 2. I pray you, or beseech you. Numb. 12. 13. So here.

*{Who ever perished}* Whoever was brought so low as you are, that was a godly man, and so near to destruction? Or, perished for want of food and other necessaries, Psal. 37. 25. Or, perished to the world, that is, was killed, and violently taken out of the world, and so was lost to men here, Isa. 57. 1.

*{being innocent}* Being not guilty of a wicked life, or of some heinous offense, that cries to heaven for judgments, Gen. 18. 20. & 19. 13. Act. 28. 4. That which Eliphaz here takes for an undoubted truth; that no good man ever died a violent death, is most false; as may appear by the example of Abel, Naboth, and many prophets and martyrs, slain by wicked men. We must not therefore take all for truth, that these friends of Job say, although some of their speeches be cited elsewhere in Scripture. True it is that they said thus, though all be not true that they said. Eliphaz might the more easily err in this, because the Scriptures, in likelihood, were not written in his time; or, at least, no part of them known to them, neither ever cited by them; and some of the examples, before quoted, were long after his death.

*{the righteous}* Men who live justly in the world.

*{cut off}* The word signifies hiding, Psal. 69. 5. Yet so, as that a thing appear no more, Exod. 9. 15. which is often done by cutting off. Righteous were never cut off by the sickle of God's judgments, before their time, as you are in danger to be, if you repent not, nor their seed utterly taken away, as yours are before your face, that so their family might be rooted out, and their remembrance utterly perish, Psal. 9. 6. For *Cut off.* See on chap. 15. 18, 28. on *Hid*, and *Desolate.*

**V. 8. ***{Even as I have seen, they that plow iniquity, and sow wickedness, reap the same}* Eliphaz sets out the great care and pains that wicked men take to do evil, by a similitude, taken from the great and continual pains of the husbandman about his corn; and their bad success, to an ill crop, arising from bad ground or seed.

*{even as I have seen}* He proves that good men are not destroyed by God's judgments, because they light upon wicked men. And that he proves out of his own experience. As far as ever I could observe, wicked men were cut off by God's hand, but never any godly man. So Numb. 11. 23. *Thou shalt see*, that is, know by experience; or, by diligent inspection, and careful observation; as Gen. 34. 1, 2. For *See* look on chap. 19. 27.

*{they that plow}* A metaphor from husbandmen. To plow iniquity, is to take pains about it; as men do about fields, that are fit to bear corn. Plowing, signifies devising and plotting various ways of sin, and satisfying their own lusts. So Prov. 3. 29. & 6. 18. where the translation reads, *devising*: but the original is, *plowing*; Prov. 21. 4. where plowing seems to be expounded by thoughts, in the verse following. Men turn up all corruptions in their hearts; and conveniences in the world, for effecting their wicked devices. For *Plow* see on chap. 1. 14.

*{iniquity}* Or, *vanity.* For sin produces no real good. For *Iniquity*, see on chap. 18. 7. on *Strength.*

*{and sow}* Bring their mischievous plots into action, Gal. 6. 8.

*{wickedness}* Or, *labours.* For wicked men toil themselves to satisfy their lusts: and take more pains to go to hell, than some do to go to heaven.

*{reap the same}* Receive the fruit, or reward of their wickedness. Gal. 6. 7, 8. Wickedness is put for the fruit of wickedness, Jer. 4. 18. as works, for the fruit of works, Rev. 14. 13. Or the words having a double signification, the meaning may be; They that plow and sow iniquity, and wickedness, shall reap vanity and labor, or trouble; Prov. 22. 8. Or the same in degree. Most wicked men are most of all punished. And so are you, O Job. Or, they that oppress others, shall be oppressed themselves, Matth. 7. 2. So the word signifies, Psal. 7. 14, 15. Isai. 59. 4. And this sin of oppression Job is charged withal elsewhere. Men look to reap good fruit of their seed and labor; to have no fruit thereof is uncomfortable, but to reap that that may poison, or hurt them, is worse. Or this verse may be knit to the next, and then the sense is, They that plot iniquity, and execute it, may reap the benefit of it for a time; yet they shall at length be consumed by God's judgments.

**V. 9. ***{By the blast of God they perish, and by the breath of his nostrils are they consumed}* He shows how wicked men come to be destroyed. And lest men should think God's judgments, like a blast, could blow none away, but light ones; like dust or feathers, he adds the strong ones, who are like lions, vers. 10. And this also he had seen.

*{By the blast}* By a sudden and secret judgment, Isai. 40. 7. Psal. 103. 16. He shows God's great power who by breathing on men, can do more than many armies; and needs no armies to effect their destruction. So corn is blasted and perishes. It may be a continuation of the former similitude, ver. 8. They look for a good crop of the iniquity they have plotted, and executed, but it will come to nothing, like corn blasted: and so do the seedsmen also, 1 Kings 8. 37. Isa. 37. 27. Amos 4. 9. Hag. 2. 17.

*{of God}* The powerful, or puissant one, who, with a blast, can destroy them.

*{perish}* See on ver. 7.

*{by the breath of his nostrils}* Spoken after the manner of men, whose breath is in their nostrils, Isa. 2. 22. for God has no nostrils. Creatures when they are angry, breathe more fiercely at their nostrils, Acts 9. 1. The word sometimes signifies wrath, which is showed by the foresaid breathing, Isai. 30. 33. Psal. 27. 12. & 54. 10. 2 Sam. 22. 16. And it may be read, *by the breath of his anger*; that is, By his angry breath. As in fire of flame; that is, in flaming fire, 2 Thess. 1. 8. He alludes to the manner of the death of Job's children, by a mighty wind, so strong, as if God himself had breathed it out, Chap. 1. 19. Wicked men are as easily destroyed by God, as a fly is blown away by a man. For *Breath.* See on chap. 15. 2. on *Vain.* For *Nostrils* see on chap. 14. 13. on *Wrath.*

*{consumed}* Not weakened only; but utterly destroyed. For *Consumed* see on chap. 36. 11. upon *Spend.*

**V. 10. ***{The roaring of the Lion, and the voice of the fierce Lion; and the teeth of the young Lions are broken}* Wicked and cruel tyrants, who by their power oppress others and persecute them, are in Scripture compared to devouring beasts, and especially to lions; who by threatenings terrify men, as lions make beasts afraid, by roaring and preying upon them, Psal. 57. 4. & 58. 4, 6. Ezek. 19. 2, 3. Prov. 28. 15. 2 Tim. 4. 17. It may be he meant Job, and accuses him of cruelty and impatience; in regard of his former life and complaints, and so makes him like a devouring, or roaring lion; and his sons like young lions. There are seven names of lions observed in Scripture, whereby some writers conceive their ages to be distinguished. 1. גור *a Lions whelp*, Gen. 49. 9. 2. כפיר *a young Lion*, Ezek. 19. 3. 3. ארי and אריה *a grown Lion*; of ראה *to see*; because lions are quick-sighted; and as some some affirm, sleep with their eyes open, as watching for a prey. Or, of ארה *to tear in pieces*, signifying a lion that begins to prey upon cattle: or of ירא *to fear*, because then men begin to fear him, that were not afraid of him when he was little, or young; Isai. 11. 7. & 38. 13. 4. לביא of לב *a heart*; and it notes out a hearty stout lion, because it is the most courageous of all the creatures, Isai. 30. 6. Numb. 23. 24. 5. ליש *an old Lion full of yeares*, Prov. 30. 30. 6. שחל *an huge Lion*, Job 28. 8. 7. שחץ *a proud Lion*, that goes proudly. Many of these are together, Job 28. 8. Gen. 49. 9. Nah. 2. 11, 12. Others make אריה a common name to all lions, as *homo* in Latin a man. גור as an infant: כפיר, as a young man: לביא, as a male: ליש as a man at full growth, and perfect age.

*{the voice}* This word in Scripture, sometimes signifies a sound, where no intelligible words are, as Job 15. 21. and thunders are called, in the original, *voices*, Exod. 9. 28. Rev. 4. 5. For *Voice* see on chap. 15. 21. on *Sound.*

*{the teeth of the young Lions}* The strength of oppressors, who grind others as young lions tear their prey with their teeth. For *Teeth* see on cap. 16. 9.

*{are broken}* Or, *pulled out.* Though they be young, and the time not yet come, that their teeth should shed for age, yet they shall be pulled out, that they may no more prey on the beasts. The sense is, That God by his judgments, weakens oppressors in the midst of their days, and takes away their strength and craft, that they may no more hurt others, Psal. 3. 7. & 58. 6.

**V. 11. ***{The old Lion perisheth for lack of prey, and the stout lion's whelps are scattered abroad.}*

*{The old Lion}* See on ver. 10.

*{perisheth}* See on ver. 7.

*{for lack of prey}* What oppressions have gotten, is taken from them, and they and theirs perish, because they can get no more; as lions starve themselves and theirs, when they cannot prey abroad, Psal. 59. 14, 15. & 109. 10, &c.

*{the stout Lions}* See on ver. 10. *Whelps.* Heb. *Sons.* See on chap. 14. 21.

*{are scattered abroad}* To get food, yet can find none, which is the cause why the old lions die, because they cannot go to seek food themselves, nor their young ones find any for them, or themselves. Or are scattered abroad, that is, driven from their dens, so that they cannot come there to feed themselves, or the old ones.

**V. 12. ***{Now a thing was secretly brought to me, and mine ear received a little thereof}* He had endeavored before to prove out of his own observation, that none but wicked men were so punished, now he goes to prove it by vision.

*{Now}* Heb. *And.* So this particle is translated, Dan. 10. 14.

*{secretly brought}* Heb. *brought by stealth.* Unseen or heard by others, as thieves steal, and convey their stolen goods away by dark. Such was this vision, in the night, as follows, ver. 13. So Christ comes to judgment unlooked for, as a thief in the night, 1 Thess. 5. 2, 4. 2 Pet. 3. 10. Rev. 3. 3. & 16. 15. If you will not believe me, believe the vision, wherewith God has honored me, in conveying secretly to me, a thing I knew not before, and not affording it to others. So Paul heard the voice of Christ, which his companions did not, Act. 22. 9. Whether this vision appeared to Eliphaz now, (upon occasion of Job's troubles, musing that a man, esteemed so good, should be so afflicted) or formerly, the Scripture has not revealed.

*{mine ear received}* A periphrasis of hearing. I heard it. The ear is said to receive what it hears. So a man receives meat, when he eats it, Act. 9. 19. & 27. 34, 35, 36. For *Ear* see on chap. 15. 21. For *Received* on chap. 15. 12. on *Carry away.*

*{a little thereof}* Chap. 26. 14. a small part, not all. Either because it was like a low voice, which he could not perfectly hear, or because it suddenly passed away, so that he could not have time to lodge it all in his memory. See his modesty. He does not say it was fully revealed to him, but in part, being mindful of man's weakness, who sees but in part in this life, 1 Cor. 13. 12.

**V. 13. ***{In thoughts from the visions of the night, when deep sleep falleth on men}* The former verse showed the manner how, this verse shows the time when this vision came to him.

*{In thoughts}* Heb. *boughs*, Isai. 17. 6. Thoughts shoot out from the soul in great variety, as boughs from trees. And when store of thoughts trouble men's heads, God speaks to them, sometimes by dreams and visions, Dan. 2. 29. & 4. 5.

*{from the visions}* Arising from the visions. Or, *after the visions.* So this particle is translated, Hos. 6. 2. *after two dayes.* Or these words may be knit to the words following, and the meaning be; not that these thoughts, here mentioned, came from the vision ensuing, but from the fear mentioned, ver. 14. Visions did differ from bare revelations, because what was in them revealed, came from some visible shape, presented to men.

*{of the night}* To distinguish them from such visions, as were seen in the day. God did sometimes appear in some visible representation to his servants, when they expected it not. And that sometimes in the day, Gen. 18. 1. & 19. 1. and sometimes in the night; for then are men's minds more free to receive revelations, and contemplate of them, being free from the noise and businesses of the day. So Dan. 2. 19. Numb. 22. 19, 20.

*{when deep sleep}* An exact description of the dead of the night; and the word signifies an extraordinary sleep, or dead one, as they call it, Gen. 2. 21. Jon. 1. 5. It seems to be in the former part of the night, for the first sleep is the deep sleep, especially to men wearied with cares, or labors of the day. The meaning may be, either when other men were fast asleep, and I awake and musing; or when I was asleep, as well as they. So that he might see this vision either sleeping, or waking.

*{falleth}* For vapors, as they first go up to the head, and make it drowsy, so they fall from thence like a great weight upon all the members of the body, and oppress all the spirits and senses, and cast men into a sleep.

*{on men}* Heb. *on sorrowfull men*, as the derivation of the word imports. Men wearied with the labors of the day past; for it is observed, that poor laboring men sleep soundest, Eccl. 5. 12.

**V. 14. ***{Fear came on me, and trembling, which made all my bones to shake}* Now he sets down what effect this vision had upon him, before he heard any words. A kind of terror lights on men in the beginning of divine revelations, partly to make them reverence the special presence of God's spirit, and partly to make them acknowledge their own inability to discern such mysteries, Isa. 6. 5. Dan. 10. 8. Rev. 1. 17. Job 7. 14, 16, 19.

*{Fear came on me}* Heb. *met me.* Inwardly in my soul, arising from the unusual sight of a spirit, mentioned ver. 15. For *Met* see on chap. 13. 22. on *Call.*

*{and trembling}* Outwardly in the members of my body, which shows the fear of the mind to be great, that made all the body shake, Psal. 2. 11. Exod. 15. 15. Dan. 10. 11. Hab. 3. 16.

*{all my bones}* Heb. *the multitude of my bones.* And so it is translated, Chap. 33. 19. The most of them. Or, *All my bones*, being many. See Rom. 5. 15, 18. My fear was so great, that it stayed not in my flesh, but pierced into, and wrought upon my very bones, and made those firm pillars of my body to shake. So God terrifies men in the beginning of visions, to humble them, that they may respect his majesty and receive his message with the more reverence; and afterwards he comforts them, Luk. 2. 9, 10. For *Bones* see on chap. 2. 5.

**V. 15. ***{Then a spirit passed before my face, the hair of my flesh stood up}* He goes on to describe the terror this vision brought upon him, that Job might give the more credit to it, as coming from God.

*{Then}* Or, *When.* For it seems to be the cause, as well of his former fear, vers. 14. as of this here. So this particle signifies, Dan. 10. 4. *as*, or, *when I was by the side of the great River.*

*{a spirit}* An angel. The angels are so called, Psal. 104. 4. A good angel, for God uses not to employ evil ones in acquainting his servants with his will: yet was this spirit in some bodily shape, else could he not have seen it, Luk. 24. 37, 38, 39. Or, *a wind.* So the same word is translated, Chap. 1. 19. & 1 King. 19. 11. yet not likely to be so here, because the wind neither stands still, nor has any image, as this apparition did and had, ver. 16. For *Spirit* see on Chap. 15. 2. on *vain.*

*{passed}* Went suddenly away, as a forerunner of the following vision, yet came back, ver. 16. Or, *changed.* The word may import a variable motion, forward, backward, upward, downward, like a wind wheeling about, Eccl. 1. 6. not progressive like a man's motion. First it moved up and down, and then stood still. This made him fear the more.

*{before my face}* In my sight. For *Face* see on chap. 14. 20. on *Countenance.*

*{of my flesh}* Of my body. So flesh is taken, Gen. 2. 24. Psal. 119. 120. or, of my head, which is a part of the body, by a synecdoche of the whole for the part; as man is mortal, that is, his body must die. For *Flesh* see on chap. 14. 22.

*{stood up}* By reason of his great fear, and the coldness of his external parts arising thence, and straitening and pressing the roots of his hairs, they stood upright.

**V. 16. ***{It stood still, but I could not discern the form thereof; an image was before mine eyes, there was silence; and I heard a voice, saying}* This is a description of the preparation for the revelation ensuing.

*{It stood still}* To wit, the spirit, ver. 15. It went not presently quite away. It passed up and down before, vers. 15. now it stands still, as presenting itself to him to be seen, or as ready to speak somewhat to him. Men make a stand, when they speak. So do preachers and orators. An ambulatory voice is not so easily heard.

*{I could not discern the form thereof}* There stood as it were an obscure shadow before me, whose form I could not fully and distinctly discern, nor judge well of the whole frame of it: Or, it may be his former fear did so astonish him, that he observed not this image, nor could make nothing of it distinctly, yet he could hear the words.

*{an image}* Some kind of bodily likeness, but not so exact, that I could take full notice of it.

*{there was silence}* I kept silence, being a little freed from my fears, and when all things were quiet, I attended to hear what the spirit would speak, Rev. 8. 1, 5.

*{and I heard a voice}* Uttering the following words to the end of the chapter. Others join both parts of the sentence together, and read it thus, *I heard a still voyce.* Heb. *I heard stillnesse and a voyce*, 1 King. 19. 12. for, *a still voyce*: as, *a cloud and smoke*; for, *a smokie cloud*, Isai. 4. 5. For *Hear* see on chap. 13. 17.

**V. 17. ***{Shall mortal man be more just than God? shall a man be more pure than his Maker?}* The voice spoke the words following for Eliphaz his instruction.

*{Shall?}* Certainly he shall not. An affirmative question often includes a negative answer, Matth. 16. 26.

*{mortal man}* Wretched man subject to sins and miseries. The word signifies man's weakness and inability, to stand before God.

*{be more just then God}* Or, *be just rather then God*, Luke 18. 14. If a man would call God to account, and charge him with injustice in laying too heavy punishments upon him, and God should permit that man himself to be judge, if he would judge by the rule of right reason, God would get the day, and his justice be cleared from all brands of injustice, that that man could charge him with, and the man himself would be forced to confess, that he suffers for his sin, and was unjust in accusing God. This was God's sense in likelihood, who spoke to Eliphaz; but it may be Eliphaz might misapply it, (as himself, and his companions do many other grounds) to prove Job an hypocrite. As if he meant, that he must be forced to think Job more just than God, if God should lay such great plagues upon one that was not an hypocrite or an oppressor.

*{Shall man}* A word that imports strength, in the original, by the derivation of it. The strong man can no more stand before God than the weak one. This is added, lest any one should think weak men indeed cannot stand before God, but strong men may.

*{be more pure then his Maker?}* If he lay the fault of his sufferings on his creator, he should be like a servant, that speaks ill of his master, or sues him for injury done to him, when as he ought to submit to him; much more man, to God his maker. Psal. 39. 9. Rom. 9. 20. For *Make* see on chap. 14. 9. on *Bring forth.*

**V. 18. ***{Lo, he put no trust in his Servants; and his Angels he charged with folly}* He shows, that there is no comparison between angels, and God; much less between man and God. Or *Behold*, A note of admiration: Look at it as a wonder. See on chap. 1. 12. A thing worthy to be noted, to confute your insolency, who accuse God. Or, *Behold if:* A note of certainty. So Deut. 13. 14. & 19. 18. If the thing appear certainly true upon inquiry. See on v. 3. of this chapter. No marvel, if he find great matter of correction in you, who found it in his best servants the angels.

*{he put no trust}* Or, *He could put no trust.* He did not, nor could not credit, nor confide in those persons mentioned. So the word signifies, Prov. 31. 11. Job 15. 15. And the Greek word answerable to it, Joh. 2. 23, 24. He saw some of them would be disloyal, not to be trusted on: And found some of them afterwards not faithful, (as the word sometimes signifies, Num. 12. 7. Heb. 3. 5. 1 Cor. 4. 2.) and such as would have failed him if he had trusted in them; for they fell afterwards. 2 Pet. 2. 4.

*{His Servants}* Not the prophets, or best men, as some think, because these servants are opposed to men, who have earthly bodies, in the next verse. It must needs therefore be meant of angels, who are called God's servants, wait on him, and are employed by him, Psal. 103. 20, 21. & 104. 4.

*{He charged}* Heb. *Put.* That is, he accused them judicially, and found them guilty.

*{with folly}* Or, *with madnesse.* The word signifies shining light. Thence, praising men for their virtues shining in them. And on the other side boasting of a man's own good parts, and so folly and madness. Or, *Nor in his Angels in whom he put light.* Or, *Though he put light in his Angels.* If the angels whom God made in so glorious a condition fell from it, man made in a lower condition, cannot justify himself before God. Or, *Though he put praise in his Angels;* That is, matter of much praise, by making them so excellent.

**V. 19. ***{How much less in them that dwell in houses of clay, whose foundation is in the dust, which are crushed before the moth?}* The three following verses show the opposition between angels and men, consisting in two differences. Angels are spiritual substances, men are bodily. Angels are immortal, men are mortal. No wonder then, if men cannot stand, where angels fell.

*{How much less}* It agrees with the former part of the former verse. If he put no trust on angels, much less on men. Or, *How much more.* And then it depends on the latter words of the verse precedent. If he charged his angels with folly, then much more men.

*{that dwell}* That abide and remain.

*{in houses of clay}* In bodies made of a corporeal and baser matter than the angels, and therefore less trust to be put in them. The body is called the house and tabernacle of the soul, 2 Cor. 5. 1. And it is made of clay or earth, Esai. 64. 8. Job 10. 9. & 13. 12. & 33. 6. The soul is the man that inhabits this house of clay. The mind is the man. And we read in Scripture of the inward man, 2 Cor. 4. 16. and the inward man of the heart, 1 Pet. 3. 4. Others interpret it of those houses we dwell in here, which are made of an earthly matter, and nothing like the glorious heavens wherein the angels dwell. So the angels' sin is aggravated by their habitation, Jude vers. 6. Others take it for graves, wherein we lie long, when we are dead. The grave is called an house, Job 17. 13. & 30. 23. Isa. 14. 18, 19. The Egyptians called graves eternal houses; and other houses here inns, because we abide much longer in them, than in these, Diodor. l. 1. p. 33. For *Houses* see on chap. 15. 28. The Heb. word חמר signifies 1. A heap, Hab. 3. 15. 2. A measure called an omer. Exod. 16. 16. 3. Clay, or dust. Job 13. 12. So here.

*{whose foundation is in the dust}* This is true of men themselves, in respect of their bodies, which were made of dust at first, Gen. 2. 7. and therefore called dust, Gen. 3. 19. Psal. 103. 14. and said to return to the dust, Gen. 3. 19. Job 34. 15. to go down to the dust, Psal. 22. 29. to sleep in the dust, Dan. 12. 2. If man's body were made of clay, yet had it a good foundation, it might stand long, as a weak house may, Matth. 7. 25. but the foundation is weak here: Light dust, (not solid earth) which is more subject to scattering than clay. Therefore having so weak a foundation, it cannot last long. True also of their houses, which have as an earthly matter, so an earthly foundation. For *Dust*, see on chap. 14. 8. on *Ground.*

*{which are crushed}* Heb. *they crush them.* Impersonally. So Luk. 12. 20. *They shall require thy soule*, that is, it shall be required.

*{before the moth}* Sooner than a moth can be crushed between a man's fingers; which is killed only with a touch, and a very light one also. Or, *in presence of a moth.* He is not able to stand out against the weakest creature, but wastes insensibly, and by degrees, as a garment moth-eaten. So vers. 20. God gives them up as food for vile creatures. See the phrase, Psal. 39. 11. Isa. 50. 9. & 51. 8. Hos. 5. 12. Or, *Before consumption.* So the root of this word is translated, Psal. 6. 7. & 31. 9, 10. They die for the most part, before their natural strength be spent. Most men by some inward diseases, or outward calamities, perish in the strength of their age, or before: Few live to be very old, Psal. 90. 10.

**V. 20. ***{They are destroyed from morning to evening, they perish for ever, without any regarding it}* Some understand the former verse of a natural death, and this of a violent one: yet both may be true of either kind of death.

*{They are}* Or, *Which are*, and so the text may go on in the same tenor with the former verse.

*{destroyed}* Heb. *beaten to pieces*; as in a mortar.

*{from morning to evening}* Suddenly, or quickly, within the compass of an artificial day. Or *from sun to sun*; that is, between sun-rising, and sun-setting, Psal. 90. 5, 6. Isa. 38. 12. Their life is very short, like those creatures that begin and end their life in a day. Or, *every day*, or, *all day long*, some die, Psal. 44. 22. Rom. 8. 36.

*{they perish for ever}* They are quite taken out of this world, never to return hither again, while the world lasts, Chap. 16. 22. Psal. 39. 13. They shall dwell no more in this world, Job 14. 14. Psal. 103. 16.

*{without any}* Scarce any consider of it, so few, that they are scarce worthy to be named, in comparison of them that do it not. See the like phrase, Joh. 1. 10, 11, 12. & 3. 32. Isa. 57. 1.

*{regarding it}* Heb. *putting.* To wit, his heart to it, or laying it to heart. So Isa. 41. 20. it is translated, *consider.* No man perceives their end so nigh. It is beside the opinion, and beyond the expectation of all men. Or, No man, no not themselves take notice of this fleeting condition, to repent in time: or, No man is troubled at their death, or looks further after them, to see what is become of them, Isa. 57. 1. Or, it may be read thus, *without any considering, that they die for ever.*

**V. 21. ***{Doth not their excellency, which is in them, go away? they die even without wisdom}* In this verse is declared the great power of death over us, in that it spoils us of our best parts.

*{Doth not}* Certainly it does. A negative question, often includes an affirmative answer, 2 Sam. 11. 3.

*{their excellency}* Gen. 49. 3, 4. whatsoever is esteemed excellent in them. Some take it for the reasonable soul dwelling in the house of clay, vers. 19. Luk. 12. 19, 20. Psal. 146. 4. It may be called their excellency, because by it they are made more excellent than the beasts. Others take it for wisdom, or beauty, strength, riches, or honor, or any good thing wherein one man exceeds another. The goodliness of them, Isa. 40. 6. Not only the house of clay, and foundation of dust, but also all the adorning, painting, and polishing of it, with the rich hangings, and precious furniture thereof, decays, Psal. 39. 11. & 49. 12.

*{go away}* Or, *remove*, Numb. 13. 11. The journeys; That is, the several removes of the people. Or, *Is taken away*, to wit, by God, or by death; for it is in a passive form in Hebrew, Isa. 38. 12. *Mine age is departed*, or taken away: Suitable to that which follows there, *and is removed from me.*

*{and not with wisdom}* Their wisdom cannot keep them from death. They have no more privilege against death than fools, Psal. 49. 10. 2 Sam. 3. 33.
