Or, *not wisely*, that is, with abundance of folly; as, not holding guiltless, for holding very guilty, Exod. 20. 7. Or, they cannot carry so much as their wisdom away with them, Chap. 36. 12. Or, They prepare not wisely for death, but die, as if they had no wisdom, nor knew not what belonged to life or death, Psal. 49. 20. If by excellency, be meant riches; then by wisdom, may be meant skill to get wealth in another world, as they did in this.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1. ***{Call now, if there be any that will answer thee, and to which of the Saints wilt thou turn?}* Hitherto were the words spoken to Eliphaz in the former vision, contained in the five last verses of the fourth chapter: now he returns to his former discourse, and to what he moved Job to, Chap. 4. 7. to see, if ever any saint were so afflicted. This is confirmed by that which follows; as if he had said to Job, You show yourself an hypocrite by your impatience under troubles, vers. 2. And God confirms it by laying such troubles on you, as he does on wicked men, vers. 3. &c. See if ever any saint were so impatient, or so afflicted. Many wicked men have.

*{Call now}* Not by exclamations, as Isa. 58. 1. nor by invocation, as Psal. 50. 15. but by nomination, Matth. 20. 8. Search the records, and take notice of the name of every saint departed, or alive, and see if any be like you. Call any good man that ever lived by his name, and if he would answer you, he would confess that he was never so afflicted, and that God never deals so with any as he has done with you, except with hypocrites, and ungodly men. For *Call* see on Chap. 13. 22. For *Now* see on Chap. 4. 7. on, *I pray thee.*

*{that will answer thee}* That will resolve you of the truth in this matter. Or, *that is answerable to thee.* So the word is used, Prov. 27. 19. Eccl. 10. 19. whose condition is like yours. And if there be none like you in sufferings or carriage, then you are no saint, but an hypocrite. For *Answer* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Of the Saints}* Persons separated from the world, and set apart to serve God. So the word is used, Lev. 27. 10, 14, 15, 16. Such are the saints, Joh. 15. 19.

*{wilt thou turn?}* Or, *on which of the Saints wilt thou look?* As a painter turns often from the picture to him, whom he is to set out, to see if it be like him. Not bare turning about is here meant, but turning to observe the countenance of another; for the same word פנה, signifies to look on a thing: and thence comes פנים, *a face.* On what saint will you fix your eyes to find a parallel for yourself? Among wicked men or fools you may, vers. 2.

**V. 2. ***{For wrath killeth the foolish man, and envy slayeth the silly one}* None of the saints will be of your mind; but they will all acknowledge, that God's wrath lights on ungodly men only.

*{For}* Besides the testimony of the saints, the thing itself appears to be so. Or, *Surely.* It is a thing generally known, and needs no proof. So this particle is used, Prov. 1. 17.

*{Wrath}* Impatience under God's judgments, and fretting at them, as you, O Job, have done in an high nature, Chap. 3. Or, God's wrath. Wicked men by their sins give God cause to make them miserable; good men do not.

*{killeth}* Uses to kill. God commonly by some extraordinary death destroys ungodly men; especially such as being under God's judgments for their sins, condemn God of injustice, and therefore he destroys them. Or, Their own vexing spends their spirits and kills them: or, Their impatience thrusts them on things unlawful or dangerous, that have death attending on them.

*{the foolish man}* Him that does things rashly for want of wit. Wicked men are in Scripture often called fools, Psal. 107. 17. Prov. 1. 32. & 8. 5. & 9. 13. & 14. 1, 17. The Hebrew word is the same with the English in sound, *Evil.* Such use not their knowledge well, and therefore are as if they had none, Zech. 11. 15. Eccl. 7. 9. It is an argument of folly to hurt themselves by sinning, and not to understand the causes of their punishments, that so they might amend, and be freed from God's judgments. Such an one Eliphaz accounts Job to be, though others accounted him wise. As wicked men are fools in print, so on the contrary in our old English books, a righteous man is printed, a *right wise* man; and righteousness, *right wisenesse.* For it is true wisdom, Psal. 111. 10. Prov. 1. 7. as sin is mere folly, Gen. 34. 7. 2 Sam. 13. 12, 13.

*{envy}* At other men's prosperity. As if this judgment had come upon Job for envying other men's prosperous condition. Or, *jealousie.* That is, God's wrath, who is a jealous God, Exod. 20. 5.

*{the silly one}* Who like a child is easily persuaded to wickedness for want of judgment in himself. So much the word imports, as coming from a word that signifies to persuade, or allure, Hos. 7. 11.

**V. 3. ***{I have seen the foolish taking root; but suddenly I cursed his habitation}* He proves the former proposition of the downfall of ungodly men, from his own observation.

*{I have seen}* Or, *I my self have seen.* It was a thing I took special notice of, Chap. 4. 8. Psal. 37. 35. I hope I may trust mine eyes, Numb. 16. 14. Lest any man should object again, Chap. 4. 8. that wicked men flourish sometimes, he answers, I have seen that also, that ungodly men have thrived and carried themselves loftily, as you have done, but they have soon been cast down. For *Seen* look on Chap. 19. 27.

*{the foolish}* See on vers. 2. Luk. 12. 20. Or, *a foolish man.* Meaning some one man of note.

*{taking root}* Like a great tree that has in a long time taken deep rooting, spread its root far and wide, and so fastened itself, and flourished the more, Psal. 37. 35. Hos. 14. 5. So have I seen wicked men, settled in their habitations, having gotten a great, sure and firm estate, as themselves and other worldly men thought, and put out children as fair branches, vers. 4. He could not see the roots of the trees: But his meaning is, that as a man that looks on a stately tree shooting forth plenty of boughs, knows it has firm rooting, else it could not so spread itself: So wicked men could not live in such state, if they were not very rich; nor would they, if they thought their wealth would not abide. See Isa. 11. 1.

*{suddenly I cursed}* Even when he was in his height, and no evil like to befall him, I judged him unhappy for all his wealth, and foretold his sudden destruction, which I knew God would speedily bring on him, and his, Prov. 3. 33. The word נקב literally signifies to strike through one, as with a dagger, Hab. 3. 14. Isa. 36. 6. Metaphorically it signifies to strike one through with a curse. Yet it rather signifies foretelling of evil, than wishing it, as Numb. 22. 17. & 23. 8, 9, 11. Cursing there, is foretelling evil; as blessing is foretelling good. The event also is included. It came so to pass. So Psal. 4. 6. & 85. 8. Rom. 1. 17, 18. Gen. 22. 12. And as here foretelling is put for the event, so elsewhere the event for the prediction, Jer. 1. 10. Lev. 13. 3, 8. *Pronounce him unclean.* Heb. *defile him*, Isa. 6. 10.

*{his habitation}* So the word נוה signifies, Chap. 8. 6. Isa. 32. 18. Jer. 23. 1. & 33. 10. Sometimes it is put for a place of pasture, Psal. 23. 2. Joel 2. 22. Sometimes for the cottages of the shepherds, where they dwell quietly, Zeph. 2. 6. Jer. 25. 37. It includes not only his dwelling-house, but his family also, and his children, vers. 4. and his provisions for his family, vers. 5. Prov. 3. 33. Dan. 11. 4. He thought himself surely seated, which made his destruction the sorer, Psal. 30. 6, 7.

**V. 4. ***{His children are far from safety, and they are crushed in the gate, neither is there any to deliver them}* Now he sets out the curse what it was, and that it was not idle, or in word only, but reached both himself and his posterity. It may be some of his children lived then in misery, for he seems to set out a particular man for an example, and that within the compass of his own observation, vers. 3.

*{far from safety}* In extreme danger. As Far from light is, In extreme darkness; so Far from God's law, is, Extremely wicked, Psal. 119. 150. Far from peace, in a most desperate condition, Lam. 3. 17. Far from oppression, in a most safe condition, Isa. 54. 14. As wicked men are far from safety, so safety is far from them, Psal. 119. 155. Isa. 59. 11. By *safety*, is here meant, help, and things needful to preserve men's lives, or keep them out of danger.

*{they are crushed}* As a moth between one's fingers. See on Chap. 4. 19. 2 King. 7. 20.

*{in the gate}* Publicly and openly all men looking on. For what is done in the gate, is known to all, because of the multitude of passengers, that go in and out, Psal. 9. 14. Prov. 31. 31. Gen. 23. 10. & 34. 20. Jer. 7. 2. & 22. 2. Or, They are called to an account, and cast in judgment, and lose the cause and the day. For judgment was given in the gate, that all might hear it, Ruth. 4. 1. Deut. 22. 15. Amos 5. 10. Prov. 22. 22. Psal. 127. 5. Job 29. 7. & 31. 21. Isa. 29. 21. They have many troublesome suits, and always are condemned, which is a great misery and curse, Psal. 1. 5. & 109. 7. The word שער signifies, 1. The gate of a city. Gen. 34. 20. 2. The city itself. Jer. 15. 7. 3. The place of judicature which was usually in the gates. Deut. 17. 5.

*{any to deliver them}* They are forsaken of all, and have neither advocate to plead for them, nor judge to deliver them, nor any friend to sue for a pardon, which other men have, Psal. 109. 12. Either to show, that none can resist, where God will punish men for wickedness, as Hos. 5. 14. Psal. 50. 22. Or, that all men will turn away from those whom God leaves; or, that the power of the judges was so great, that none dared help those whom they condemned. Thus they, whose fathers pitied none, find none to pity them, Psal. 109. 13, 14.

**V. 5. ***{Whose harvest the hungry eateth up, and taketh it even out of the thorns, and the robber swalloweth up their substance}* In this verse he sets out the curse of God both upon the foolish man, mentioned vers. 3. and on his children mentioned vers. 4. For *whose*, is singular in the original, and *their*, plural. He must lose his harvest, and his children their substance.

*{whose harvest.}* All the income of his labors. As devouring widows' houses, Matth. 23. 14. It cost him much labor in plowing and sowing, and he expected a great crop, but it was suddenly taken from him. For *Harvest* see on chap. 14. 9. on *Boughes.*

*{the hungry eateth up}* Some understand it of his heirs, who being kept short while their father lived, waste all when he is dead: so that ill-gotten goods seldom come to the third heir. Others, of men oppressed, and impoverished by him, who when he dies, come and take all away by force, which he intended to leave to his heirs, and so make themselves amends for their goods taken away by him. But it may well be understood of any spoilers in general, who come like hungry people, devouring all, and leaving nothing to the possessors. As greedy, as if they never had enough. Like fire that devours all, Chap. 1. 16. Isa. 29. 6. or like the sword, that sweeps all away, Deut. 32. 42. Or like wild beasts, that eat up all, Gen. 37. 33. Or like persecutors, who eat up God's people as bread, Psal. 14. 4.

*{taketh it even out of the thorns}* His hunger makes him fear no scratching nor danger, but takes it by force, as if a man should gather corn out of the midst of thorns without fear of pricking. Or he may allude to the custom of their times, and of ours also, to make hedges both about standing corn, and about ricks of gathered corn, Exod. 22. 6. Hos. 2. 6. Prov. 15. 19. Isa. 5. 5. Mic. 7. 4. Psal. 80. 12. & 89. 40. And then the sense is, Though he make hedges of thorns to keep his corn from robbers; yet hungry persons will break through those hedges to get it. They will have it whatever it cost. We say in the proverb, Hunger breaks through stone walls. Or, *Taketh it to a place out of the thornes.* Where he may quietly eat it. Or, *It comes to him that taketh it out of the thornes.* And some words may be omitted, because it not being written in prose, the verse would be too long else. For *Taketh* see on Chap. 15. 12. on *Carry away.*

*{the robber}* Chap. 18. 9. The word is taken from another word, which signifies long hair; because thieves living in deserts, and not regarding their dress, let their hair grow like wild men; or, to hide their faces, that they might not be known. Or, *the thirsty*; who are in want, and thirst naturally, or after other men's goods; as hungry before, in this verse. Neither wet nor dry provisions, vineyards, nor cornfields, could escape these robbers.

*{swalloweth up}* With great greediness, as thirsty men drink up all to the bottom in one breath: or as wild beasts, or whales swallow up lesser creatures, Psal. 56. 2. Jon. 1. 17.

*{their substance}* The word signifies power, strength or activity, Gen. 47. 6. and so an army, which is strong; and lastly riches gotten and kept by strength, and counted men's strength, Prov. 10. 15. and making them powerful. For *Substance* see on chap. 15. 29.

**V. 6. ***{Although affliction cometh not forth of the dust; neither doth trouble spring out of the ground}* He now directs Job to look up to God for help; because afflictions have an higher cause, than men ordinarily look at, and are not to be avoided by humane policy.

*{Although}* Or, *Because*; as 1 Chr. 13. 9, 11. 2 Chr. 6. 24. Or, *seeing*; as Chap. 28. 21. *Affliction*; the fruit of sin. Or, *Iniquity*; the cause of punishment. For so also the word signifies, Psal. 5. 5. Hos. 4. 15. For *Affliction*, see on chap. 18. 7. on *Strength.*

*{cometh not forth of the dust}* A proverbial speech in likelihood used in those days, and in that place, to show that troubles came not by chance, nor was to be imputed to secondary causes, but some greater reason might be given of them: Either that they came from our sins as the meritorious cause, or from God as the efficient. Both which considerations should make men seek to God as offended with their sins, and inflicting those punishments upon them. For *Cometh forth* see on chap. 15. 13. on *go out*, and on chap. 20. 25. For *Dust* see on chap. 14. 8. on *ground.*

*{Trouble}* Or, *Labour*, toil arising from sin. For the word signifies both labor, and pain; as both the Greek and Latin also answering to it. *Out of the ground.* As weeds do that grow out of the ground without man's work or care. God's hand sends them, and man's sin brings them.

**V. 7. ***{Yet man is born unto trouble, as the sparks fly upward}* Having showed in the former verse whence man's troubles do not come; now he shows, whence they do come. The word אדם signifies, 1. The first man, Gen. 2. 20. 2. Any man, Prov. 3. 13. So here.

*{Yet}* Or, *because*; or, *seeing.* See on vers. 6.

*{Man is born}* Breeds himself trouble, as easily and ordinarily, as if he were born for that end, as he is born in sin, Psal. 51. 5. which breeds all his troubles: They mistake the sense and scope of the place that apply it to men's working in their particular callings, though it be true that men are born not to idleness, but to employment. The like mistake is of that passage, Gen. 3. 19.

*{unto trouble}* Or, *labour.* See vers. 6.

*{As}* Heb. *And.* So this particle is used, Chap. 16. 21. & 34. 3. It is sometime put for a note of likeness, and then it couples things most strictly together; showing not only that they are both true, but also one like another. It is as natural for man to work himself trouble, as for sparks to fly upward.

*{The sparks fly upward}* Heb. *The sons of the quick or live coal lift up to fly.* So we find a son of the bow, for an arrow, Job 41. 28. sons of the quiver, for arrows, Lam. 3. 13. the son of the floor, for the corn, Isa. 21. 10. And the next word translated, *quick or live coal*, Isa. 6. 6. If you blow the coal, the sparks will fly upward. So man, if he be once born, he will have trouble, Job 14. 1. עוף signifies, 1. To fly. Deut. 4. 17. 2. To be faint. 1 Sam. 14. 31. Here it is taken in the first sense.

**V. 8. ***{I would seek unto God, and unto God would I commit my cause}* Having showed that afflictions take not their rise from secondary causes, he exhorts Job to return to God by repentance, who being powerful, and merciful would pity him then, and restore him to his former happy condition. To this purpose, in the residue of the chapter, he speaks much of God's power and wisdom, both in the works of nature, in general; and especially in his works about mankind.

*{Surely}* This word is in the Hebrew; but is omitted in the translation. See on Chap. 1. 11.

*{I; I myself}* For besides the person included in the verb, the pronoun is added to set an emphasis on it. See on vers. 3. He propounds it in his own person in a friendly manner, that it might the better go down with Job. So does Paul, 1 Cor. 3. 6. with 4. 6. propound his own example, that other ministers might not think much, if their labors were fruitless sometimes when the apostles would be so always, if God did not give the increase. So Eliphaz, here; *If I were in thy case and these corrections lay on me, I would seek to God.*

*{would}* I would not have fretted, nor cursed my day as you have done (Chap. 3.) but I would choose a better way. Think you of your holiness as you please, I would acknowledge God's providence in these troubles, and confess my sins, and seek to God for delivery.

*{Seek to God}* By prayer, for counsel, help, deliverance, as to one higher and stronger than I, that so I might find favor with him, 2 Chr. 16. 12. Psal. 9. 10. & 34. 4. & 77. 2. Isa. 9. 13. Zeph. 1. 6.

*{unto God}* One name used for God in this verse before, is taken from strength: the other here used is that which is communicated to judges, Exod. 21. 6. Psal. 82. 1, 2. To show, that God had justly inflicted these troubles on Job, and none could deliver him out of God's hands; as vers. 4. and therefore God must be pacified by prayer, and entreaty, and it is in vain to fret against his proceedings with us, though they be harsh to us, Prov. 25. 15.

*{commit my cause}* Or, *Put my words*; That is, pray.

**V. 9. ***{Which doth great things and unsearchable: marvellous things without number.}* He sets out God's wonderful power, either to terrify Job (who as he thought, was grown insolent against God) by confirming God's providence in punishing wicked men, as he had said before. Or to comfort him in his miseries, by showing him a present remedy, from God's ability to deliver him out of the greatest troubles, and his wisdom to find an issue, when men cannot. He that is most powerful is most worthy to be feared, and to be sought to. God's powerful works are set out here in general, and afterwards in many particulars in things natural and civil, to the advancing of the poor, that their spirits may be raised, and their enemies daunted.

*{which doth}* At present, and continually, Joh. 5. 17. For *Doth* see chap. 14. 9. on *Bringeth forth.*

*{great things}* Not like men, who with much art and labor produce trifles. Men's greatest works are nothing in comparison of God's. Great things in quantity; as hills, seas, whales, elephants: but especially in quality, and excellency. Jer. 10. 6. Psal. 136. 6, 7. So the moon is called a great light, Gen. 1. 16. though some stars be bigger, because in regard of the nearness of it to the earth, it looks bigger, and gives more light. Some interpret these great things, of God's ordinary works in the course of nature, and oppose them to the wonders following. Others take both words promiscuously; for wonders above nature are called great things, Luke 1. 49. and there are wonders in the course of nature, Psal. 136. 5. Psal. 136. 4, 5, &c.

*{And unsearchable}* Heb. *And there is no search.* They may be searched into, but not with any expectation to find out the causes of them, or the manner of doing of them by the wit of man, though he search never so strictly. That labor is in vain. Therefore *unsearchable* is expounded by *past finding out*, Rom. 11. 33. It signifies also high, deep things, and secret things, which men cannot come at, as the height of heaven, the bottom of the sea and of the earth, the heart of kings, Prov. 25. 3. Job 38. 16.

*{Marvellous things}* Such things as we cannot understand the cause of, because it goes above the capacity of our natural reason. The word signifies not only things that we admire, as not knowing the causes of them, but also anything hidden; or above the reach of our understanding, Judg. 13. 18. Psal. 131. 1. It is derived from a word that signifies *dividing*, or *separating.* Exod. 33. 16. It imports things divided from our understanding, as miracles are, which are above the course of nature; and many things also in the course of God's ordinary providence, for he speaks not here of creation, as appears by the instances following. These are difficult things, and such as we use to wonder at; Deut. 17. 8. Gen. 18. 14. God does most magnificent works, which do far exceed our strength and capacity.

*{without number}* Heb. *till there be no number.* There is such a multitude of God's marvelous works, that they cannot be numbered by man, Gen. 15. 5. Rev. 7. 9. but God can number them, as he can the stars, Psal. 147. 4. Or, it may be spoken by a figure called hyperbole, to show that they are very many. See the like, Exod. 3. 8. Deut. 1. 28. Psal. 107. 26. Joh. 21. 25.

**V. 10. ***{who giveth rain upon the earth, and sendeth waters upon the fields}* He shows God's power first in the works of nature.

*{who}* He puts the demonstrative article before in the Hebrew; as if he would say, I mean that God, who gives rain. He did not so in the former verse; nor does not in the verses following, because here he begins to enumerate God's particular wonderful actions.

*{giveth}* He instances in such works, as none can do but God. The earth would be fruitless, if God did not send rain, Jer. 5. 24. For *Giveth* see on chap. 14. 4. on *Bring.*

*{Rain upon the earth}* He begins with this as a wonderful and profitable work; not abiding at all times, as the sun, moon, and stars, for then the world would be drowned, but sent by God at divers seasons, and in divers places, especially where rivers are scant, as they were in those hot countries: and this was a special sign of God's providence for the refreshing of the earth, and earthly things, that else would be dried up. And it is more observed and admired, than the constant light of the sun; and it is called the husband of the earth by the Hebrew writers, because the earth can no more bear fruit without rain, than a woman children, without company of a man. It is therefore reckoned among God's mighty works, Chap. 28. 26. Jer. 10. 13. Amos 5. 8. Act. 14. 17. For *Earth* see on chap. 9. 24.

*{sendeth waters}* Rain from heaven, as before. Or, floods, by rain falling abundantly; or by rivers overflowing their banks. And that either in judgment to destroy the fruits of the earth, or in mercy, where rain is not usual to fructify the land, as in Egypt by Nilus overflowing, Deut. 11. 10, 11. Gen. 2. 5, 6. For *Sendeth* see on chap. 14. 20. For *Waters* on chap. 14. 9.

*{upon the fields}* Heb. *upon the out-places*, Psal. 144. 13, 14. that are to the towns, as streets are to the houses, more open places. Such as have no covering overhead to keep the rain from falling on them, nor no walls to enclose them, as houses and towns have. So bountiful is God, that he not only waters the tilled grounds, but also, wildernesses and deserts, where no man comes, nor no husbandman looks for fruit. This shows God's magnificence, whose bounty takes care for vilest things, and makes wildernesses, and inaccessible hills green, that wild beasts may have food, as well as men, Job 38. 25, 26. For *Fields* see on chap. 18. 17. on *Street.*

**V. 11. ***{To set up on high those that be low, that those which mourn, may be exalted to safety}* This is added to encourage Job to humble himself before God, who was able to restore him to his former prosperous estate again.

*{to set up}* The connection shows the meaning, that God by sending rain gives plentiful crops, and so making their fields fruitful, raises the poor husbandmen, first to riches, and then to honor. Or the sentence may begin here, *To set up, &c.* He disappoints. &c. vers. 12. God makes room for the preferment of the poor, by ruining the mighty.

*{on high}* An allusion to judges and kings, who sit on seats and thrones higher than others, 2 Sam. 3. 10. 1 King. 1. 30, 35, 48. So higher seats in feasts and public places, were provided for, and given to persons of greatest eminency, 1 Sam. 20. 25. Prov. 25. 6. Matth. 23. 6. Mark 12. 39. Luke 1. 52. & 11. 43. & 14. 8. So on the contrary, to be in a low estate, or humbled, is set out by sitting in the dust, 1 Sam. 2. 2, 8. 1 King. 16. 2. Psal. 113. 7. Isa. 47. 1. or in a low place, Eccles. 10. 6. Jam. 2. 3. The meaning is, to prefer mean men to riches, honor, and authority.

*{those that be low}* In their outward estate, Luke 1. 48, 52. Psal. 107. 41. and accordingly of low esteem among men, and set below others. He may mean poor husbandmen, who for want of rain are well nigh undone.

*{those which mourn}* Heb. *those which are black*; to wit, in face: for sorrow will make men look swart, Jer. 4. 28. Lam. 4. 8. Or in apparel, for mourners used to wear black, as a sign of sorrow, Ezek. 31. 15. As on the contrary, men in prosperity, and joyful times, did wear white, Eccl. 9. 8. Esth. 8. 15. He may have respect also to husbandmen, who mourn for want of rain, and make their faces swart by hard labor in the sun.

*{exalted to safety}* Lifted up into an high place, where they may be safe; as if they were lifted into an high tower, where no weapons could reach them. Hereby is intended delivery out of their former wants, freedom from them for time to come, and open and lasting honor, that cannot be taken from them, Prov. 29. 25.

**V. 12. ***{He disappointeth the devices of the crafty, so that their hands cannot perform their enterprise}* He shows how he makes low ones safe: by crossing plots of mighty ones, invented for their ruin. It is also a further instance of God's power in things civil, which was showed before in things natural. He had showed God's power before; now he sets out his wisdom.

*{He}* Or, *who.* That it may run on in the same phrase, and answer to the ninth and tenth verses.

*{disappointeth}* The word, in another form, signifies breaking, but in the form here used, making void, frustrating, disappointing, Psal. 119. 126. Ezr. 4. 5. & 9. 14. for when the engine is broken, men cannot effect their plots. God disappoints the devices of politic men, one while by hindering them from bringing their devices to the end they aim at, and desire: as follows in this verse. They cannot perform their enterprises. So that all their endeavors and purposes are to no purpose, Isai. 14. 24, 25. Psal. 33. 10. Otherwhile, by contradicting and resisting them, Numb. 30. 8. 1 Pet. 5. 5. Jam. 4. 6.

*{the devices}* Conclusions in men's minds, after long and serious thoughts. Curious devices, as the word imports; like curious the girdle, Exod. 28. 8. So that not bare thoughts are here intended, but plots, and decrees, and witty inventions resolved and concluded upon in their minds, which they thought could not be hindered from execution, and by which they thought to raise themselves, and pull down others. As God lifts up some, who were so low, that they looked for no preferment, vers. 11. so he disappoints those that plot for it by the ruin of others, Gen. 50. 20. Psal. 56. 5, 6, 7. Esth. 9. 24, 25.

*{of the crafty}* Sometimes the word is taken in a good sense, Prov. 1. 4. & 8. 12. & 22. 3. but most commonly in a bad sense, for such as use their wisdom to hurt others, Gen. 3. 1. Psal. 83. 3. and so here. Some think the word signifies, one that has many turnings and windings in his mind, as the serpent had in his body, Gen. 3. 1. It comes from a word that signifies naked, because such as show tricks of activity, use to strip themselves naked. The same word signifies naked, and subtle, Gen. 2. 25. & 3. 1.

*{so that}* Heb. *and.* It is used in this sense, Chap. 7. 15, 20. & 41. 16.

*{their hands cannot perform}* The hands are the instrument to act, as the tongue is to speak what the heart invents. For *Hand* see on chap. 15. 23. For *Perform*, on chap. 14. 9. on *bring forth.*

*{their enterprise}* Which they had craftily contrived, thinking it could not be disappointed, yet they could not effect it, Neh. 4. 15. Isai. 8. 10. They could not give their plots an actual being, or substance; for so the word signifies. For *Enterprise* see on chap. 26. 3. on *The thing as it is.*

**V. 13. ***{He taketh the wise in their own craftiness: and the counsel of the froward is carried headlong}* In the former verse he showed, that God did ruin the plots of ungodly men; now he shows how, to wit, by making their own devices to recoil on their own heads; and not only work liberty to good men, but also ruin to themselves.

*{He}* Or, *who.* See on vers. 12.

*{taketh}* When they think that they have, by their policy, entrenched themselves in the castles of their craft, so sure, that no man can touch them, God takes them by a greater policy, as men do strong castles, or as hunters do wild beasts in gins, and carries them away, as prisoners taken in war, Numb. 21. 1, 32. Deut. 2. 34. 2 Sam. 12. 27. Gen. 31. 26. Isa. 20. 4.

*{the wise}* Wise to do evil, for craft is ascribed to them here, and ver. 12. They may be called wise, because they think themselves so. Such as can work out anything, as they think; and that none can prevent them. So the Greek word signifies, 1 Cor. 3. 19. where the Apostle quoting this verse, as Scripture, shows the whole book to be canonical.

*{in}* That is, *by.* It notes the instrument, as Psal. 33. 6. Heb. 1. 1, 2.

*{their own craftiness}* In their witty devices, whereby they thought to ruin others. So did Haman perish, Esth. 9. 25. God uses their craft to their own destruction, as if a strong man should beat back the enemies' weapons upon himself, whereby he intended to hurt others, Jer. 21. 4. Or it may be a metaphor taken from catching of birds in nets, or snares. God uses their crafty devices, intended as nets to catch others, for to catch themselves, Psal. 7. 15, 16. & 9. 15, 16. & 35. 7, 8. & 141. 10.

*{the counsel}* Device, as ver. 12. Psal. 81. 12. Or, plot devised by many, laying their heads together, Psal. 1. 1. & 2. 2. Isai. 19. 11. Counsels are ordinarily taken for results of serious debates with others. Crafty devices may be the fruit of one man's brain.

*{of the froward}* Deceitful persons, who have one crafty device twisted within another, like a rope. The word signifies to wrest or turn a thing, and then to wrestle. Such as can turn themselves every way, as wrestlers do to get advantage of others, and wind themselves within them, to overthrow them by policy, when strength fails, Gen. 30. 8. Deut. 32. 5. Psal. 18. 26. Prov. 8. 8.

*{is carried headlong}* It comes from a word that signifies haste; for hasty folks take not time enough for advice, but do things very rashly, and with precipitation, and so not only miss what they intend, but also have ill success, Isai. 59. 14. and 32. 4. The word מהר signifies, 1. To do a thing speedily. Psal. 16. 4. 2. To give a dowry. Exod. 22. 16. 3. To do a thing rashly, as hasty men use to do, Isa. 32. 4.

**V. 14. ***{They meet with darkness in the day time, and grope in the noon day, as in the night}* The reason is here set down of their bad success; want of knowledge, though they think themselves wise.

*{they meet with}* Or, *run into.* It meets not with them, but they bring troubles upon themselves by their rashness: or find unexpected difficulties hindering the success of their counsels.

*{darkness}* Some take it for troubles, which are often set out in Scripture by darkness, as prosperity is by light. But by darkness, we may rather understand ignorance how to effect their designs.

*{in the day time}* They meet with trouble in their prosperity. Or rather, God sends a spirit of giddiness upon them, Isai. 19. 3, 14. so that with all their search and counsel, they cannot see things that are clear, and might easily be dispatched, but go on slowly, and fearfully, as men in the dark. They have light without, but want light within.

*{grope in the noon day, as in the night}* They are like blind men, who at high noon, when the sun shines brightest, grope for doors and walls, as other men do in the darkest nights, Gen. 19. 11. Judg. 16. 26. Deut. 28. 29. Isai. 59. 10. Lam. 4. 14. The meaning is, that these crafty men meet with troubles, in the midst and height of their prosperity. Or rather, in things most clear, they seek out for some way to execute their wicked devices, and are so besotted, that they can find none.

**V. 15. ***{But he saveth the poor from the sword, from their mouth, and from the hand of the mighty}* The Scripture uses presently after threatenings, to annex promises, lest men should think, that God were more ready to kill, than to save, Psal. 34. 16, 17, 21, 22.

*{But}* See on Chap. 1. 11.

*{he saveth}* Keeps from troubles, or delivers out of them.

*{the poor}* Such as are in great want, and so are forced to make known their wants by uttering their desires. For it comes from a word, that signifies willing, or desiring.

*{from the sword}* From war, and the miseries attending it, which threaten their life, vers. 20. Gen. 31. 26. Psal. 144. 10.

*{from their mouth}* From the mouth of those crafty ones, mentioned, vers. 12, 13. From their plots, who like wild beasts would swallow them up, Psal. 124. 3, 6. Or *from the sword*, that is, *from their mouth*; or, *that cometh out of their mouth.* That is, from false witnesses, or slanders, that may endanger their life, or credit, Prov. 12. 6. & 18. 8. & 26. 22. Or, from sentence of death given against them by unjust judges. Or, from bad counsels given against them by ungodly men, and virulent words, whereby they are pierced, as with a sharp sword, Psal. 57. 4. & 64. 3. Jer. 9. 8. & 18. 18. Or, *out of their mouth:* The oppressors cannot keep close their plots, but reveal them to others, whereby they come to be disappointed. For *Mouth* see on chap. 15. 5.

*{From the hand}* God not only defends them from their reproaches, but also from their violent attempts to take away their goods, life, or liberty. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{of the mighty}* Of the oppressor, who being more powerful than he, would overcome him, if God did not assist him.

**V. 16. ***{So}* By this means he concludes the discourse of God's wonders, with the fruit of them. They breed hope in the oppressed, and shame in the oppressors.

*{the poor}* One that is exhausted or drawn dry, as rivers, when men take the fish, or turn them from fenced cities, Isa. 19. 6. One that had means and strength, but now has lost them. And such are worst able to bear poverty.

*{hath hope}* He that is now delivered, in another trial when he shall be forsaken of all men, has hope that God will deliver him again, 1 Sam. 17. 37. 2 Cor. 1. 9, 10. Or, another poor man seeing God has delivered this man, will hope for the like, Psal. 32. 6, 7. Or, Being delivered, he has the thing he hoped for, that is, safety or deliverance, Psal. 32. 10. See hope, for the thing hoped for, Rom. 8. 24. Col. 1. 1.

*{iniquity}* Every wicked and ungodly man; as pride, for the proud man, Jer. 13. 9. and on the contrary, righteousness, for righteous men, 2 Pet. 3. 13. Ungodly men, who are unjust and unequal in all their ways, and oppress the poor, Psal. 71. vers. 4.

*{stoppeth her mouth}* Shuts it, and speaks not proud and high words, as formerly. They shall blush, and be confounded, wonder and be astonished, when they shall see poor men, whom they thought none could deliver out of their hands, strangely delivered, and taken away from them, and shall not dare to murmur against God, having nothing to say against the justice of his proceedings; and therefore he does not say that God stops their mouth, but themselves, Psal. 107. 42. Mic. 7. 16. Jer. 8. 14.

**V. 17. ***{Behold happy is the man whom God correcteth: therefore despise not thou the chastening of the Almighty}* Here Eliphaz proceeds to another argument, to move Job to submit to God's hand, and acknowledge his sin, in regard of those deliverances that God gives to such, as make a good use of afflictions.

*{Behold}* A note of attention, and admiration, that men in trouble should be happy, contrary to the judgment of the world, that counts them miserable. See on chap. 1. 12.

*{Happy is}* Heb. *The blessednesse of the man.* They consist in these deliverances and favors that follow. So Prov. 3. 11, 12. Jam. 1. 12. Heb. 12. 5, 6. Rev. 3. 19. Yet those places seem to speak of those crosses, whereby good men are tried; but this of such troubles, as bring wicked men home to God. Psal. 94. 12, 13.

*{The man}* The word signifies sorrowful, or miserable man, according to the original of it. He is miserable by nature, and in himself, but made happy by God's grace.

*{whom God corrects}* Sometimes the word signifies reproving, which is a verbal correction; as Lev. 19. 17. But here it imports a real correction by stripes and troubles, Chap. 33. 16, 19. Either he means, that God's correcting wicked men for their sins, is a way to bring them to blessedness, by driving them to repentance: or else he includes the use of corrections. The man, whom God by affliction, brings out of his wicked ways, shall be blessed. For *Correct* see on chap. 13. 3. on *Reason.*

*{therefore}* Heb. *and*; It is a conclusion out of the former premises; If it be a happy thing to be afflicted, that we may be drawn from our sinful courses, then you must not despise God's corrections. So this particle is used as a word of inference, Chap. 21. 14. Dan. 9. 11.

*{despise not thou}* Prov. 3. 11, 12. Heb. 12. 5, 6. Do not set light by it, or contemn it, as a thing of no use, Numb. 11. 20. Psal. 53. 6. & 118. 22. Isa. 8. 6. & 30. 12. but make great account of it, as of an excellent means of happiness, Psal. 51. 17.

*{the chastening}* It is not the sword to cut off, but the rod to correct: therefore despise it not.

*{Almighty}* Or, *All-sufficient*, Gen. 17. 1. who is able to turn afflictions to our good, and sufficient to himself, and to all creatures without the help of any of them, Chap. 6. 4.

**V. 18. ***{For he maketh sore, and bindeth up: he woundeth; and his hands make whole}* He gives a reason, why God's corrections should be highly esteemed; because, though they seem hurtful, yet they are for our good: and so it may be translated, *Though he maketh sore, yet he bindeth up.* For the comfort is not in smiting, but in healing. So is this particle used, ver. 5, 6. Dan. 9. 9. and the other is translated, *yet*, Dan. 9. 13.

*{He maketh sore}* Or, *Though he make sore, yet his hands*, &c. as before. So *Though*, is added out of the former branch, Job 16. 6. Hos. 6. 1. A similitude taken from a surgeon, who though he could forbear making an incision; yet does it for the healing of the patient, and afterward cures the wound. So does God wound our bodies, to heal our souls, and then heals our bodily wounds also.

*{bindeth up}* As a skillful surgeon carefully binds up a wound; as a broken arm or leg, to heal it, Isa. 30. 26. Ezek. 34. 4. Psal. 147. 3. The word חבש is used. 1. For binding on ornaments. Exod. 29. 9. 2. For binding on plasters, Ezek. 34. 16. 3. For keeping back, as it were by a band. Job 28. 11. 4. For saddling or binding horse-trappings, Gen. 22. 3. Here it is taken in the second sense for binding on plasters to heal.

*{he woundeth}* Deut. 32. 39. Heb. *smiteth.* He strikes hard, so that he makes a wound. Wounding is more, than making sore; and making whole, is more than binding up, Psal. 68. 21.

*{his hands}* He goes on with the similitude of a surgeon, who makes his plasters, and lays them on with his hands; and so may be said to heal with them. For *Hand* see on chap. 15. 23.

*{make whole}* Do not only give ease, but perfect the cure.

**V. 19. ***{He shall deliver thee in six troubles; yea, in seven there shall no evil touch thee}* Now he describes the blessedness aforesaid, by particular deliverances out of sundry troubles.

*{He shall deliver thee}* He shall snatch you out of the enemies' hands, or out of danger.

*{in six}* In many troubles: a certain number, for an uncertain; according to the usual phrase of Scripture, Amos 1. 3. Luke 17. 4. Isa. 4. 1. 1 Sam. 2. 5. Jer. 15. 9. Prov. 30. 15, 18. & 6. 16. & 24. 16. Others understand it of the particular dangers enumerated afterwards: Famine, war, slander, destruction by other means, wild beast, stones of the field, tame beast: seven in all. Some take it for deliverance out of them. They might come on him, but not destroy him; because he says in six; not from six, 1 Cor. 10. 3. Others take it for keeping him from those troubles that fell on other men; Psal. 91. 7. Rev. 3. 10. 2 Pet. 2. 7. Neither could they be delivered out of destruction, which is one of the kinds mentioned, but must be kept from it. For there is no help for them that are destroyed: and it follows, *No evill shall touch thee.*

*{Troubles}* The word signifies straits. A similitude from a man, whose body girt too strait, puts him to much anguish and pain, 2 Sam. 24. 15.

*{yea}* Heb. *and.* So it is translated, Prov. 6. 16. and 30. 18.

*{in seven}* If a seventh trouble come, he will keep you out of that also. He will not only free you from many, but from all troubles; Psal. 91. 3. & 34. 19. & 18. title.

*{Touch thee}* It shall not hurt you. See Chap. 11. 1. Psal. 105. 15. Or, it shall not so much as lay hold on you; but you shall be free, when others are oppressed with famine, &c. Psal. 91. 10.

**V. 20. ***{In famine he shall redeem thee from death; and in war from the power of the sword}* He comes from the general, to particulars, to assure the truth of it.

*{In famine}* A sore judgment, Ezek. 6. 11. Jer. 24. 10. and the first great evil here mentioned. In time of famine, when it falls on others, you shall have food enough, Psal. 33. 19. & 34. 10. & 37. 19. Famine arises ordinarily from the barrenness of the earth, or too much, or too little rain, or from war, which hinders sowing, and carries away the crop from the owners.

*{Redeem thee}* Make a difference between you, and others, Exod. 8. 22, 23. You shall be free from the wants, that others feel in famine; as a captive redeemed with a price, is free from those bonds, that others feel. Or, *rescue*; in general; as Psal. 107. 2.

*{from death}* You shall not starve, as others do.

*{In war}* In time of war. This is the second trouble: War and famine often go together, Jer. 18. 21. For in war, provisions are wasted, and sowing is neglected. It comes from a word that signifies to eat, for the sword devours, 2 Sam. 11. 25. Jer. 12. 12. & 46. 10, 14.

*{from the power}* Heb. *From the hands.* For as men's strength is showed by their hands; so also, what we have in our hands, is in our power; Jer. 18. 11. Psal. 63. 21. So Psal. 49. 15. Hos. 13. 14. 1 Sam. 17. 37. Psal. 22. 20.

*{of the sword}* It shall have no power to kill you, as it does others.

**V. 21. ***{Thou shalt be hid from the scourge of the tongue; neither shalt thou be afraid of destruction when it cometh}* He proceeds to the third and fourth troubles, slander, and violent death.

*{Thou shalt be hid}* When ungodly men accuse others, they shall forget to accuse you. Or, You shall be as safe, as if you were in an hidden place, where none could find you. God shall protect you, so that a lying tongue shall not hurt you, Psal. 31. 20.

*{from}* Heb. *in.* Or, *when the tongue scourgeth.* When it scourges other men.

*{the scourge}* From slanders and reproaches, by which other men's credit is wounded, as the body is by scourging, Prov. 14. 3.

*{of the tongue}* From thence comes a word in the original, that signifies to slander, Psal. 101. 5. and to accuse, Prov. 30. 10. For *Tongue* see on chap. 15. 5.

*{shalt thou be afraid}* So as to distract you, or make you despair, or give over looking after means of deliverance, Heb. 11. 7. Exod. 9. 20. Isa. 7. 2. Jer. 20. 3. Or, You shall not be in any danger at all, so much as to fear any hurt.

*{of destruction}* By thieves or robbers breaking in, and killing others, or of any violent death.

*{when it cometh}* Upon others. God will keep you, that you shall not be destroyed, when your neighbors are; as he did Noah and Lot: And if not afraid when it comes, much less before, at the rumor of it, Matth. 24. 6. Luke 21. 9. For *Come* see on chap. 15. 21.

**V. 22. ***{At destruction and famine thou shalt laugh: neither shalt thou be afraid of the beasts of the earth}* He repeats two of the former troubles, as being most terrible, yet with a phrase that imports a greater freedom from the fear of them; and then goes to the fifth trouble: For those that would make these distinct calamities from the former, bring in needless subtleties, which the text affords not.

*{At destruction}* See on vers. 21.

*{and famine}* See on vers. 20.

*{thou shalt laugh}* An higher privilege than redemption from the one, ver. 20. or not fearing the other, vers. 21. You shall be so far from that, that you shall laugh at them. It is not a laughter of derision of God's judgments, but of joy, that God preserves us, when he destroys others. Yet emphatically expressed, to show the great safety and confidence of God's people; as if a soldier should see the bullets kill many by him, and stand it out fearless and joyful, persuading himself they shall not hurt him. Or like one contemning and laughing at his enemies' preparations, or not thinking them worthy to be regarded. Or one in a dry house smiling in a great storm, that makes others without doors mourn, Chap. 39. 7, 21, 22, 25. Psal. 2. 4. & 37. 13. Prov. 31. 21, 25. Hab. 1. 10.

*{shalt thou be afraid}* See on vers. 21. Isa. 11. 6, 7, 8, 9.

*{of the beasts of the earth}* This is the fifth trouble from which a penitent sinner shall be free. *Beasts of the earth*, may include all beasts, as made of the earth, and living on the earth. But being here distinguished from *the beasts of the field*, ver. 23. it may be taken for wild beasts, as it seems to be, Deut. 28. 26. Jer. 7. 33. for such use to eat man's flesh. Wolves nor lions shall not hurt your flock nor you, Psal. 91. 13. 1 Sam. 17. 34, 35, 36. These may well be called *beasts of the earth*, because they range all the world over to seek for prey; whereas tame beasts are kept in pastures, and may therefore be called *beasts of the field.* For *Earth.* See on chap. 9. 24.

**V. 23. ***{For thou shalt be in league with the stones of the field; and the beasts of the field shall be at peace with thee}* The sixth and seventh troubles are here mentioned, which might arise from stones and tame beasts.

*{For}* Or, *Surely.* As this particle is translated, Chap. 8. 6. & 20. 20. For it is a new deliverance, and not a reason of the former. Lest he should think, the wild beasts are far off, and I need not much fear them, but I may be hurt by stones, and tame beasts, he says, Surely, these shall not hurt you neither.

*{thou shalt be in league}* You shall not need to fear hurt from the stones, no more than men do from such as are at league with them, though they be stronger than they. See this phrase, Isa. 28. 15. Hos. 2. 18. Ezek. 34. 25.

*{with the stones of the field}* You shall not be hurt by stumbling at them, Psal. 91. 12. nor by going barefoot on them, as many did at that time in those stony countries. Much less shall you be slain by them, as Abimelech was, Judg. 9. 53. & Goliath, 1 Sam. 17. 49. and many malefactors under the law stoned, Lev. 20. 2. & 24. 14, 16, 23. For *Stones*, see on chap. 14. 19.

*{the beasts of the field}* The tame beasts that use to graze in the field. See on vers. 22. Such creatures though made to serve man, and not so wild as others, yet by reason of man's sin, do oft forget their duty, and do him hurt. Some have been slain by horses, bulls, dogs. Men's corn and vineyards have been much hurt by them. But they shall not hurt you.

*{shall be at peace with thee}* Carry themselves peaceably to you, and do you no harm, although some of them want no strength to do it, and do hurt evil men. Or, *shall be made to be at peace with thee.* For so much the word in that conjugation it is in the original, may import. Although by man's sin, they be in some sort set at liberty, yet they shall be so overruled by God's providence, that they shall do you no harm at all.

**V. 24. ***{And thou shalt know, that thy Tabernacle shall be in peace, and thou shalt visit thy habitation, and shalt not sin}* Freedom from evils went before: now follow promises of good. A comfortable life, in this verse; a numerous posterity, vers. 25. A long life and happy death, vers. 26.

*{thou shalt know}* By sense and experience. You shall find it so indeed to your comfort, Eccl. 8. 5. *shall feel no evill.* Heb. *know.* See in the margin there, 2 Cor. 5. 21. Phil. 3. 10. Numb. 14. 34.

*{Thy Tabernacle}* It is called a tabernacle, because they dwelt commonly in tents fit for removing, according as they needed pasture for their cattle, and the heat of that country was so great, that men could endure such dwellings. Or, because men dwelt in tents at first, before they had art to build houses, and therefore their houses afterwards were called tabernacles, Chap. 8. 22. Psal. 84. vers. 1. with vers. 4. & 78. 51. & 67. Herein is included his house, household, and household stuff.

*{in peace}* Your family shall live peaceably with you, and one with another. Or, All yours shall be safe, and all you have shall prosper. So the word is taken, Gen. 29. 6. *Is he well.* Heb. *Is peace to him.* See the margin there: 1 Sam. 25. 6. 2 Sam. 20. 9. Isa. 42. 25. & 32. 17, 18. Or, *That peace is thy Tabernacle*, You shall as it were dwell in peace.

*{thou shalt visit thy habitation}* The word *visiting*, is taken sometimes for taking especial care of one, and bestowing some special favor on him; as Gen. 21. 1. Ruth 1. 6. Psal. 8. 4. & 80. 14. Sometimes for punishing men for their sins, Exod. 32. 34. Psal. 59. 5. But here it is looking into the state of his family, requiring an account of his children and servants, (as masters of families use to do) how they have carried themselves, and how all things committed to them prosper, Psal. 17. 3. Job 31. 14. as a shepherd visits his flock, Prov. 27. 23. Zech. 10. 3. Such a visit Paul gave the brethren, Act. 15. 36. And God visited Sodom, Gen. 18. 20, 21.

*{shalt not sin}* Or, *shalt not erre*; or, *misse.* See Judg. 20. 16. You shall not be deceived of your expectation, but shall find things, as you think, and as you would have it, and that yours have been faithful, and your affairs prosperous.

**V. 25. ***{Thou shalt know also, that thy seed shall be great, and thine off-spring as the grasse of the earth}* From present blessings in his life, he proceeds to blessings that would last after his death also. You shall know. By experience. You shall live to see your children's children, and leave a great posterity behind you, Psal. 128. 6. Gen. 50. 23. See on ver. 24.

*{also}* You shall have more blessings than one, Psalm 128.

*{thy seed}* Your children that come immediately out of you. They are called *seed*, because they come of the parents' seed. You have lost many children, you shall have many again, if you be reconciled to God, Chap. 21. 8. & 27. 14. Isa. 48. 19. For *Seed* see on chap. 39. 12.

*{great}* Come to great preferment. Or, *much.* You shall have many children.

*{thine off-spring}* The Hebrew word signifies twigs, or sprouts; for posterity springs from predecessors, as branches from trees, or vines, Psal. 128. 2, 3. Hereby are meant children's children, and all their posterity, except their own immediate sons and daughters, which are set down before under the name of seed, Chap. 21. 8. & 27. 14. Isa. 22. 24. & 48. 19.

*{as the grasse of the earth}* A numerous posterity is set out sometimes by the grass, that covers all the field, and which no man can number. You shall have a large posterity, which shall dwell here and there, and fill the earth like grass, Psal. 45. 16. & 72. 16. This is set out sometimes also by the sand of the sea, Gen. 15. 5. & 22. 17. Isa. 48. 19.

**V. 26. ***{Thou shalt come to thy grave in a full age, like as a shock of corn cometh in, in his season}* From blessings in life, he comes to set out his blessed death at length, after the sight of his children's children.

*{thou shalt come}* Or, *thou shalt go.* So the word signifies, Jon. 1. 3. Gen. 10. 19. & 25. 18. For *Come* see on chap. 15. 21.

*{to thy grave}* Heb. *to the grave.* See on Chap. 7. 9. You shall die in peace, 1 King. 14. 12, 13. Job 21. 32.

*{in a full age}* Not taken away by a violent death before your natural strength be spent, Gen. 25. 8.

*{as a shock of corn}* As the corn stands out against all storms of wind and rain, and at last is made up into shocks.

*{cometh in}* Heb. *ascendeth*, Psal. 102. 24. For the corn cut off the earth, is first set up in shocks, and then carried into the barn. For *Ascend* see on chap. 20. 6.

*{in his season}* When it is full ripe, and not before, as wicked men are taken away in their sins, Psal. 129. 6, 7. & 102. 24. Isa. 38. 10.

**V. 27. ***{Lo this, we have searched it, so it is, hear it, and know thou it for thy good}* He began with a fair preface, to win Job to hearken to his counsel, and now concludes with a fair hortatory persuasion to the same purpose.

*{Lo this}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{we}* He calls his friends to witness the truth of what he had said, who by their present silence, and future speeches suitable to his, do confirm it. My friends here know it as well as I. So he began in the plural number, Chap. 4. 2.

*{have searched it}* We have carefully inquired into God's providence, and found it to be so, Deut. 13. 14. Judg. 18. 2. As judges; or spies; so far were we from taking it up by tradition from our parents, or upon trust from other men, that we did not believe it, till we had found the truth of it out ourselves.

*{so it is}* Or, *it is right.* Thus this word is translated, Num. 27. 7. and should be so, Eccl. 8. 10. See notes there. We find it true, That good men perish not by God's judgments, none but bad men do. If ill men flourish awhile; yet they and their posterity are soon cut off: but if they be patient and repent, they shall flourish afterwards in the world. We are sure this is right.

*{hear it}* We have done our part in acquainting you with God's proceedings, we can do no more, your part remains to be so persuaded, and to make a good use of what we have said. Job had heard all this already with his bodily ear, but now he is exhorted to hear it with his mind, by believing it, Joh. 9. 27. with Joh. 3. 12. and yielding and consenting to it, Gen. 3. 17. and by obeying it, Isa. 55. 3. Psal. 81. 11, 13. For *Hear* see on chap. 13. 17.

*{know thou it}* Or, *do thou thy self know it.* See on vers. 8. Regard not what others do, but do you take notice of it, and apply it to yourself.

*{for thy good}* Heb. *for thy self.* For things that men learn for themselves, are for their own good; as sick men learn medicines for their own diseases; and young gentlemen study law, not to practice it, but to preserve their own estates. Prov. 9. 12. Job 19. 27. Psal. 49. 18. Repent, that you may be free from this trouble, and flourish more than before.

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1. ***{But Job answered, and said}* Now follows Job's answer to Eliphaz, in the sixth and seventh chapters.

*{But}* Heb. *And.* Though Eliphaz concluded, Chap. 4. 27. with that which he thought unanswerable, yet Job found somewhat to say against it. So this particle is used, Prov. 20. 5, 6. Or, *Then*; as Chap. 4. 1. & 8. 1. & 9. 1. & 15. 1. & 16. 1. See on Chap. 1. 11. when Eliphaz had done speaking, Job answered him, in these words following. Chap. 32. 4, 5.

*{Job answered}* To answer, is taken properly here: he answers to Eliphaz his charge and objections.

*{and said}* Spoke the words following. See on Chap. 3. 2. For all the words in this ver. see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{O that my grief were throughly weighed, and my calamity laid in the balances together}* Whereas Eliphaz accused him of impatience, he answers, that it ought to be imputed to the greatness of his grief, which was more than he could bear; and to Eliphaz his harshness, who instead of comforting him, had condemned him for an hypocrite. *Oh that.* For *If* see on Chap. 16. 4.

*{my grief}* My inward hearty sorrow, arising from my calamity. So the word is used, Chap. 17. 7. Or, *my wrath.* So Eliphaz himself uses the same word, Chap. 5. 2. whence that variety of reading, Eccl. 7. 3. & 11. 10. You think I complain too angrily, but I am not so angry as I have cause.

*{were throughly weighed}* Heb. *were weighed by weighing.* From the Hebrew word here used, comes our English word *scales*, and the *shekel*, for moneys were weighed then. See Chap. 28. 15. By a metaphor it signifies considering thoroughly of a thing in the mind, as men weigh gold, even to a grain. As if he had said, It is an easy thing to slight what men do not understand: You have but guessed at my sorrows, and not well considered of them, else would you not have censured me so deeply.

*{my calamity}* My outward troubles, the cause of my inward sorrow, Chap. 30. 13. Prov. 19. 13. The word היה or הוה, as it is written in other place, and seems by the Masorites' note over it, should be here, signifies. 1. Substance, Prov. 10. 3. 2. Mischief, Mic. 7. 3. 3. Calamity, Chap. 30. 13. So here.

*{laid}* Heb. *lifted up.* For balances are lifted from the ground, when things are weighed in them. For *Lifted up*, see on Chap. 21. 3. on *Suffer.*

*{together}* As things to be weighed are put in the balance together. You complain of my impatience, and lessen my trouble, esteeming it but a light touch, Chap. 4. 5. but I wish my trouble and grief conceived thereby, were accurately laid the one against the other, to try whether I grieve more than I have cause, or no. It would appear, that my complaint is less than the cause thereof. Or my trouble without, and sorrow within, laid in one balance, would be heavier than the sand of the sea laid in the other. So men of high degree and low, laid both together in one balance, are lighter than vanity itself laid in the other, Psal. 62. 9. See on Chap. 34. 29. for *Together* on the word *Only.*

**V. 3. ***{For now it would be heavier then the sand of the Sea, therefore my words are swallowed up}* You that account my grief lighter than a feather, would, if it had its just weight with you, find it exceeding heavy.

*{heavier then the sand}* And therefore insupportable; and no wonder then if I complain. In Scripture phrase, the sand sometimes sets out a multitude, Gen. 22. 17. Psal. 78. 27. And otherwhile it intimates the large extent of a thing, so that it cannot be measured, as Jer. 33. 22. for the sand is wider than the sea, and bounds it; 1 Kings 4. 29. It also sets out the weight of a thing, to wit, that it is most heavy; for who is able to weigh the sand of the sea, Prov. 27. 3? So here, but in a figurative sense, it imports that which troubles the mind, as an heavy weight afflicts the body.

*{of the Sea}* Heb. *of the Seas.* The sand of the sea is heavier than that of rivers, or which is digged out of the earth. Yet to make his sorrows seem the more grievous, he counts it heavier than the sand of all the seas shoveled up together. It is no wonder to hear a man groan, that has a burden on him heavier than the sand of all the seas. For *Sea*, see on Chap. 14. 11.

*{my words are swallowed up}* So the word signifies, Obad. ver. 16. Prov. 20. 25. I cannot pronounce my words distinctly, but they seem to be swallowed down, and my grief makes my voice to fail. Or I want words to express the greatness of my grief, which is unutterable. I could say much more for myself, if grief did not hinder me.

**V. 4. ***{For the arrows of the Almighty are within me, the poison whereof drinketh up my spirit; the terrors of God do set themselves in array against me}* No marvel if I complain so much, for my afflictions are not ordinary, but like poisoned arrows shot from an almighty hand, or like an army standing in battle array before me, to fight and destroy me.

*{the arrows}* The word in the original is derived from a verb that signifies to cut; (and not much unlike our English word in sound) for arrows cut where they enter, Psal. 58. 7. Arrows in Scripture, are figuratively taken for bitter words, Psal. 64. 3, 4. or for mischievous purposes intended to the hurt of others, Psal. 11. 2. Or for divine chastisements, or judgments, Ezek. 5. 16. Psal. 91. 5. & 144. 6. 2 Sam. 22. 15. Deut. 32. 23, 42. Or for afflictions outward and inward, as here, and Psal. 38. 2. These are fitly compared to *arrows*, which are swift, secret, sharp, killing instruments.

*{of the Almighty}* See on Chap. 5. 17. They are called *arrows of the Almighty*, because God, who sends them, shows his almighty power in driving them to the head, yea to the feathers, in the soul, which none else can do.

*{are within me}* They do not only hit me, but also enter into me, and stick so fast, that no flesh can pull them out. His meaning is, that no man could take away his trouble and sorrow.

*{the poison whereof}* The word signifies heat and anger, which proceeds from heat, and breathes fire, Acts 9. 1. Isa. 42. 25. Thence it signifies poison, because it heats and burns: He aggravates his sorrows, by comparing them to poisoned arrows, such as the Persians used in war, that they might kill certainly and speedily.

*{drinketh up my spirit}* Dries up my spirits, or devours them, as men do drink. My calamity goes to my heart, grieves me to the soul, and sucks out my vital spirits. Poisoned arrows dry up the spirits with their burning heat, and corrupt the blood, in which the spirits are. God's great anger against Job is hereby set out, which had well nigh brought him to the grave. For *Spirit*, see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Vaine.*

*{the terrors}* Terrible things present themselves to my imagination, and threaten further mischief to me, though I be very low already. Or, My present troubles are terrible to me. And then what he called *arrows* before, now he calls *terrors*, from an effect; for judgments terrify men. Or, He may intend, that God did fright him within in an extraordinary manner, which made his outward troubles the more heavy. Job 7. 14. & 9. 34.

*{of God}* Sent from God, Gen. 35. 5. Psal. 88. 15. 2 Cor. 5. 11. God terrifies the soul, either immediately, or by outward afflictions, Lev. 26. 16. Deut. 32. 25. Josh. 2. 9.

*{Do set themselves in array against me}* God shows that he fights against me, in that he has set so many terrible things like soldiers in battle array against me, all ready to destroy me, Jer. 50. 9. Joel 2. 5. 1 Sam. 17. 21. So men order their speeches in disputations, Chap. 32. 14. and in prayer, Psal. 5. 3. They speak methodically and exactly; as soldiers are placed for the battle, so do they place their words. The word ערך is used. 1. For setting the sacrifices in order, Levit. 6. 12. 2. To set soldiers in order for a battle, Jer. 50. 9. 3. To furnish a table, or to set dishes of meat in order on it, Prov. 9. 2. 4. To set words in order in a prayer, Psal. 5. 3. 5. To set arguments in order in a disputation, Chap. 32. 14. Here it is taken in the second sense, for setting afflictions in array like soldiers to destroy men.

**V. 5. ***{Doth the wild ass bray when he hath grasse? or loweth the ox over his fodder?}* I do not complain without a cause, for then should I be worse than the brute beasts, who will not cry out, when they have what they desire.

*{Doth the wild ass}* The Hebrew word פרא is answerable in letters, to the Latin word *Fera*, wild beasts.

*{bray}* Use to bray, Job 30. 7.

*{when he hath grasse}* Heb. *over the grasse*; as the same word is translated in the end of the verse: When he wants grass, then he does bray, Jerem. 14. 6. So may I complain, when I have cause.

*{or loweth the ox}* The former instance was taken from wild beasts, this from tame. Neither complain without a cause, and then tame beasts will complain, as well as wild. This is added, lest they should object, that Job sought to justify his wild complaints, by the courses of wild beasts.

*{over his fodder}* The word signifies mixed food: and such do we give cattle at this day, Isai. 30. 24.

**V. 6. ***{Can that which is unsavoury be eaten without salt? or is there any taste in the white of an egge?}* Nature will complain though it have meat, if it want savor or sauce; much more if it be extreme bitter. Then should I be senseless, if I should not complain of these sharp afflictions, Heb. 12. 11. Others conceive, that he strikes at Eliphaz his words, and intends that he could find no savor of right reason in them, Lam. 2. 14.

*{Can}* Do men use to eat unsavory things without salt? Or, Do they take any pleasure in doing so? For it is not impossible to do it.

*{that which is unsavoury}* See on Chap. 1. 22.

*{be eaten without salt}* Or, *Can that which is unsavoury, for want of salt be eaten?* If meat be putrefied for want of seasoning, can men take any delight in eating of it?

*{Is there any taste in the white of an egge?}* Of itself it has no pleasing taste. Or, it delights not the palate, if it be eaten without salt; as before in this verse. Yet I am forced to bear heavy afflictions, and hear foolish words: and do you wonder then that I complain so much? The Heb. word טעם signifies. 1. The taste of a thing, Exod. 16. 31. 2. Behavior, Psal. 34. in the title. 3. Advice, 1 Sam. 25. 33. 4. A decree, Jon. 3. 7. 5. Judgment, Psal. 119. 66. 6. Discretion, Prov. 11. 22. Here it is used in the first sense for taste. The word ריר signifies. 1. Spittle, 1 Sam. 21. 13. 2. The white of an egg: As here.

**V. 7. ***{The things that my soul refused to touch, are as my sorrowful meat}* Here is the application of the two similitudes in the former verse. As it is a trouble to a man to be forced to eat such things as he would be loath to touch, and he cannot eat them without sorrow; so it is a great grief to me to be forced to bear so great troubles, and hear such bitter words, and yet I may not complain. Or the words may be taken literally, as a farther aggravation of his sorrows. As if he had said, Besides my pain and loss, which is very great, I am forced with an heavy heart, for want of better provision, to eat sordid things such as I would have thought foul scorn to have touched in the days of my prosperity.

*{my soul refused to touch}* Inward and hearty dislike of them, would not suffer my hand to touch them, much less my mouth to taste of them. For the soul cannot touch anything. Not to touch a thing, notes the greatest aversation, Gen. 3. 3. For *Soul*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{are as my sorrowful meat}* It may be an allusion to mourners, and men in a sad condition, who used to eat coarse and polluted food, Psal. 102. 9. & 127. 2. Hos. 9. 4. Ezek. 4. 13. and 24. 17. For *Meat* see on Chap. 15. 23.

**V. 8. ***{O that I might have my request, and that God would grant me the thing that I long for!}* In this verse, Job sets out more plainly his desire of death, which he intimated, Chap. 3. 21. and by repeating it, shows his longing for it.

*{O that}* Heb. *who will give.* The Hebrews' manner of wishing: whereby they intimate the desire of having the thing wished for to be so strong in them, that they would be glad to take it of any one that would give it. See Num. 11. 29. Deut. 5. 29. Exod. 16. 3. Job 11. 5. & 13. 5. & 14. 13. & 19. 23. & 23. 3. Psal. 14. 7. For *Give*, see on Chap. 14. 4. on *Bring.*

*{I might have my request}* I might obtain that which I ask, and desire importunately. From this word in the original, comes שאול, the grave, one of the things that are never satisfied, Prov. 30. 15, 16. Heb. *That my request might come.* For *Come*, see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{that God would grant me}* Heb. *give me.* Yet it is well translated granting, because it was a thing much desired by Job. But a thing may be given, that is not asked.

*{the thing that I long for}* Heb. *my hope.* Or, *my expectation*, Chap. 4. 6. & 5. 16. Longing is a vehement desire of a thing, so that a man cannot be satisfied without it, Chap. 3. 21. 2 Sam. 23. 15.

**V. 9. ***{Even that it would please God to destroy me, that he would let loose his hand, and cut me off}* In vain do you, O Eliphaz, exhort me to hope for restitution (Chap. 5. 24.) for my state is altogether deplorable, and I long for nothing more, than for dissolution.

*{that it would please God}* He seems to take delight in my afflictions; would he would kill me outright. The word יאל signifies. 1. To be content, Josh. 7. 7. 2. To be willing, Josh. 17. 12. 3. To assay, 1 Sam. 17. 39. 4. To begin, Deut. 1. 5. 5. Adjure, or make to swear, 1 Sam. 14. 24. Here it is taken in the second sense, for being willing or pleasing.

*{to destroy me}* To beat me to pieces, as herbs are beaten to powder, or moths crushed, Chap. 4. 19. & 22. 9. Psal. 143. 3. Isai. 57. 15. Psal. 51. 17. The destruction here meant is death, Psal. 90. 3.

*{that he would let loose his hand}* It now seems tied, because he does not put it out far enough to make an end of me. A metaphor from a man whose hands are bound, so that he can do nothing till they be loosed. He desires, that God would show his great power in destroying him. For *Hand*, see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{and cut me off}* As trees are cut off, and then die. Or, finish my life, and make an end of it. So the word is used, Isai. 10. 12. Zech. 4. 9. Lam. 2. 17. His wealth and health were taken away before, now he wishes, that God would take away his life also, and finish his days, before the time that he might live to, in the course of nature: which the Jews call the killing or cutting off, when a man is taken away before he be fifty years old. The word בצע signifies. 1. To cut off, Amos 9. 1. 2. To be covetous, or greedy of gain, Prov. 15. 27. Here in the first sense.

**V. 10. ***{Then should I yet have comfort, yea, I would harden myself in sorrow: let him not spare, for I have not concealed the words of the Holy One}* Or; *For it should yet be my comfort, (and I would strengthen me in my grief, if he would not spare me) that I have not concealed the words of the Holy One.*

*{Then}* If I had this suit granted, the hope of an end of my pains, by death, would refresh me.

*{I would harden myself}* I would lay aside all fear of death.

*{in sorrow}* I would quietly bear the greatest sorrows in the world, so they would kill me quickly.

*{let him not spare}* I am at a point: let God lay what he will on me, so he kill me. The word imports sparing of those whom one might destroy, Ezek. 5. 11.

*{I have not concealed the words}* I have revealed them to others, for God's glory and their good, Psal. 40. 8, 9, 10. & 119. 13. Act. 20. 20, 27. For *Conceale*, see on Chap. 15. 18, 28.

*{of the Holy One}* Of God: so called, because he is perfectly holy, yea holiness itself, Hab. 3. 3. Isai. 40. 25. Hos. 11. 9. 1 Sam. 2. 2.

**V. 11. ***{What is my strength, that I should hope? and what is mine end that I should prolong my life?}* You put me in hope of a long life, but my troubles are so great, that I can bear them no longer: if they continue but a while, they will certainly make an end of me. And therefore as I have no hope of escaping, so have I no desire of living any longer.

*{What is my strength}* My strength of body is gone: I am so weak and spent, that I cannot expect long life. Or, my strength of mind is so decayed, that I begin to faint already under my troubles. The word כח signifies. 1. Force, Jer. 48. 45. 2. Substance, or wealth, v. 22. of this chap. 3. A lizard, Lev. 11. 30. Here it is used in the first sense, for force, or strength.

*{that I should hope}* Ever to get out of these troubles, or to see such good days, as you speak of, O Eliphaz, Chap. 5. 16, 25. I am too far spent to look for such a restauration.

*{what is mine end}* My death, the end of my life. Why should I be afraid of that, which will put an end to all my sorrows? Eccl. 7. 2. Psal. 39. 4. Or, the latter part of my life: it is not likely to be more comfortable than the former, Luk. 1. 26. 2 Sam. 19. 34. &c. Or, the end of my troubles. I am old, and am likely to see few good days, if my troubles were ended. And therefore the end of them is not likely to be worth my staying for.

*{that I should prolong my life}* That I should desire long life. Heb. *my soul.* Why should I desire that my soul should live any longer in this frail body? The soul is often put for the life in Scripture. See on Chap. 2. 4. Or the soul may be put here for the desire, as Prov. 23. 2. Psal. 27. 12. Eccl. 6. 9. For *Life*, see on Chap. 14. 22. on *Soul.*

**V. 12. ***{Is my strength the strength of stones? or is my flesh of brass?}* He goes on to illustrate what he mentioned in the beginning of the former verse: Think you that I am made of so hard metal, that I can endure anything? No, I am not made of stone or brass, that can bear blows, and never feel pain, or complain; but of flesh and blood, as you are, and cannot hold out long under these troubles, nor forbear complaining.

*{the strength of stones}* Equal to the strength of stones. The note of similitude is omitted; as Psal. 11. 1. Chap. 7. 9, 12. For *Stones*, see on Chap. 14. 19. *Flesh*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{of brass.}* Heb. *brasen.*

**V. 13. ***{Is not my help in me? and is wisdom driven quite from me?}* Or, *What if my help be not in me, is wisdom therefore driven quite from me?* The Chaldee paraphrase reads it thus: *Because there is no help in me, is wisdom also driven quite from me?* Do you count me a fool, because I cannot rid myself out of these great troubles? You should rather pity me; as verse 14.

*{Help}* To deliver me out of these troubles. He had no strength left to bear his troubles any longer, ver. 12. nor to help him out of them in this verse.

*{And is wisdom?}* Or, *is wisdom therefore?* So this particle is translated, Dan. 9. 11. By wisdom, he means ability, to speak for himself, and maintain his just cause. For *Wisdom*, see on Chap. 26. 3. on *The thing as it is.*

*{driven quite from me}* Expelled out of me by my troubles: a similitude taken from banished men, Jer. 40. 12. & 43. 5. You think I have lost all my wisdom, because of my bitter complaints, but you are deceived.

**V. 14. ***{To him that is afflicted, pity should be shewed from his friend; but he forsaketh the fear of the Almighty}* Here follows Job's accusing his friends of uncharitableness: you have not dealt kindly with me, as you ought to do with a man in my case. The law of friendship requires, that men should pity their friends in adversity, and comfort and relieve them by all good means they can, especially when they are well nigh consumed with troubles; but my friends fear not God, and therefore regard not me in my miseries.

*{afflicted}* Heb. *melted.* Because affliction dissolves a man's spirit, and as it were melts his heart, Psal. 22. 14. Josh. 7. 5. Therefore it is compared to fire, Psal. 66. 12. & 68. 2. Not only affliction; but fear of afflictions also melt men's hearts, Josh. 2. 11. & 5. 1. Job's heart was melted with present troubles, and fear of more.

*{pity should be shewed}* Bounty should be extended to him. More than we owe him; for to pay that we owe, is but justice. He should be commiserated.

*{But he hath forsaken}* That is, his friend, who does not pity him. It is likely Job strikes at Eliphaz, who had so much disheartened him, Chap. 4. & 5.

*{the fear of, &c.}* See on Chap. 1. 1. For *Fear*, see on Chap. 15. 4.

*{the Almighty}* See on Chap. 5. 17. Or the whole verse may be read thus; *To him that is afflicted should reproach be given, that he hath forsaken the fear of the Almighty?* Should you revile me because of my troubles, and proclaim me a wicked man: as if I had lost the fear of the Almighty? So the word is translated *reproach*, Lev. 20. 17. Prov. 14. 34. So Job answers to Eliphaz, accusing him of want of the fear of God, Chap. 4. 6.

**V. 15. ***{My brethren have dealt deceitfully as a brook, and as the stream of brooks they pass away}* Job sets out the deceitful dealing of his friends, by a comparison taken from a brook, which seems to promise much in the wintertime, and in open weather, having abundance of water in it, when there is least need, and when the rain falling plentifully, might serve to water the earth, and refresh trees, herbs, and travelers; but in frosty weather it is hidden; and in heat of summer, when there is most need, it is dried up, and suffers herbs and trees to wither, and fails the expectation of travelers. A great hurt in those hot countries, where rain is more rare than elsewhere, Deut. 11. 10, 11. So did his three friends here promise much comfort to him in time of his prosperity; but in his misery, though they seemed to come to comfort him, yet they afforded him no refreshment. That he meant them, appears by the application of the similitude, ver. 21.

*{My brethren}* They were not of the same womb, or kindred, for aught appears, but are called his brethren, either by way of friendship, or as living in the same region, as professing the same religion, 1 Sam. 1. 26. Gen. 19. 7. Rom. 10. 1. Yet it appears that some of his kindred failed him also, Chap. 19. 14. as they did David, Psal. 38. 11. For *Brethren*, see on Chap. 19. 13.

*{have dealt deceitfully}* Perfidiously, treacherously. They pretend to come to comfort me, Chap. 2. 11. but they add to my sorrows. So the word is used, Isa. 21. 2. Jer. 3. 20. בגד a garment, is of the same root; because our first parents had no garments, till they dealt perfidiously with God. Their first garments were a cover and a sign thereof.

*{as a brook}* Not a river that arises from a spring; for rivers are seldom frozen, by reason of the swiftness of the stream; and never dried up, because they are perpetually fed by springs: but such brooks as arise from rain, or melted snow; as follows, ver. 16, 17. The word נחל signifies 1. A valley, Gen. 26. 17. 2. A brook, or river, Am. 6. 14. So here. Sometimes it is used metaphorically: As, 1. To set out plenty, as there is plenty of water in a stream, Isa. 30. 33. 2. Some sudden evil carrying away all like a flood, Psal. 124. 4. 3. The failing of friends, as brooks fail in summer. So here.

*{as a stream}* Arising from a land-flood, or store of rain, which runs violently; and as it comes suddenly, so it is quickly down again, Isa. 30. 28. The Heb. word אפיק signifies. 1. A strong man, Chap. 12. 21. 2. A strong stream, or river, Psal. 126. 4. So here.

*{of brooks}* Of standing waters, which run out of their bounds, when abundance of rain comes.

*{they pass away}* Either covered by ice, under which they steal away unseen, and so become unuseful; or after a thaw, by a violent motion, they pass into a consumption. Their motion spends them, whereas the motion of a river does not waste it; for the spring still supplies it; or at the farthest, they are dried up in summer. So do my friends fail me in my adversity. For *Passe away*, see on Chap. 25. 19.
