**V. 16. ***{which are blackish, by reason of the Ice, and wherein the snow is hid}* The sum of this verse, and those that follow, to the end of the 20. ver. was given in the notes on the former verse. He shows here what is the deceitfulness of the waters spoken of verse 15. They are blackish, &c. And so deny drink to the passengers in winter for ice. Or, which being full of ice and snow, promise water to last all the year, when these are melted; but deceive men in summer. Here is the deceit, for men look not for drink from them, when they are frozen.

*{blackish}* So the word signifies, Jer. 4. 28. Though elsewhere it signify *to mourne*, Psal. 35. 14. For such go in black.

*{by reason of the Ice}* Or, *frost*; as Gen. 31. 40. We also call the frost that freezes hardest, a black frost.

*{wherein}* In which *brooks.* For the words are plural in the original.

*{the snow is hid}* The black frost brings store of ice, which swallows up the snow, which otherwise being melted, would afford drink to passengers.

**V. 17. ***{what time they wax warm, they vanish: when it is hot, they are consumed out of their place}* You would think in winter, they were lasting waters, the stream is so great; yet in summer they fail. So do my friends pretend much kindness; but fail in performance.

*{they}* The brooks spoken of, ver. 15.

*{wax warm}* When milder weather comes, and brings a thaw with it.

*{they vanish}* Heb. *They are cut off.* Then ice and snow are all gone; for the warmth and change of the weather unfreezes, and dissipates them.

*{when it is hot}* Heb. *In the heat thereof.* When the *brook* is heated in summer. Or, when the season of the year is hot. In the midst of summer.

*{they are consumed}* Heb. *extinguished.* Put out like the light of a candle, or quenched like fire. They have no more being. The heat dries the brooks quite up, whose waters remained in part after the thaw.

*{out of their place}* They are not where they were; yea their former being is quite ceased; for so much this phrase imports in Scripture, Chap. 7. 10. & 8. 18. & 27. 21. Psal. 103. 16.

**V. 18. ***{The paths of their way}* One highway may have many paths go out of it. So may a brook have many outlets. And when the waters of these brooks overflow, and run out in many bypaths, they easily perish in the midst of the fields, and come to nothing; for they are not like the rivers that run into the sea, and return with the tide, and so continue, Eccl. 1. 7. For *Way*, see on Chap. 17. 9.

*{are turned aside}* So the word signifies, Ruth 3. 8. They run out of the ordinary way, and so decay.

*{they go}* Heb. *they ascend.* See on chap. 5. 26. Water descends naturally, and rain falls on the earth; but when by the heat of the sun vapors are drawn up out of brooks, the brooks are soon dried up.

*{to nothing}* As if the world were come to the old chaos again, Gen. 1. 2.

*{perish}* Are clean gone, as if they had never been, Job 3. 3.

**V. 19. ***{The troops of Tema looked, the companies of Sheba waited for them}* He proves what he had said before; to wit, that the brooks were dried up quite, by the failing of the expectation of travelers, who having seen store of water there in the winter, expected refreshing there in the summer; but were deceived.

*{the troops}* The word signifies paths sometimes; as ver. 18. but here it notes out such as traveled in them in companies together for fear of danger, as the Turks do in caravans, Gen. 37. 25. Isa. 21. 13. & 23. 8. Judg. 5. 6. Job 31. 32.

*{of Tema}* Some conceive this place to be in Arabia the desert, and Sheba in Arabia Felix. However they seem to be places far from the sea, where they did traffic by land, and through desert places, where waters were scant. Some suppose Tema had the name from Teman, the son of Esau, Gen. 36. 11. of whom came Eliphaz, Job 2. 11. Others, more probably, from Tema, one of the sons of Ishmael, Gen. 25. 15. and so we find that the sons of Ishmael were merchants, and traveled by troops, Gen. 37. 25. This place is mentioned, Isa. 21. 14. Now these troops of Tema were either such as traveled from Tema to other places; or from other places to it. Or it may be they were such as went from Tema to Sheba; and the troops of Sheba such, as went from thence to Tema: They might be places of mutual traffic.

*{looked}* To those brooks for water, to quench their thirst, and their camels'. See the word, Psal. 34. 5.

*{the companies}* That traveled together. The former word *troopes* had its name in the original from the way, this word from walking in it.

*{of Sheba}* See above in this verse, and on Chap. 1. verse 15.

*{waited}* Till they might come thither to quench their thirst, hoping to find water there. For it is not likely they would make any long abode there, when once they saw there was no water. Or, *expected*; as Jer. 39. 11.

*{for them}* For the brooks; that is, for the water in them: as the cup, for the wine in it, Jer. 49. 12. Or, *For themselves.* That they might drink. They desired drink for their cattle; but for themselves in the first place, Gen. 24. 14, 18, 19. And they were more troubled to want water for themselves, than for their cattle.

**V. 20. ***{They were confounded, because they had hoped: they came thither, and were ashamed}* But see what followed. They finding no water there, according to their expectation, were ashamed. See the like Jer. 14. 3.

*{They}* The travelers mentioned vers. 19.

*{were confounded}* Their spirits were inwardly deaded, and they blushed outwardly.

*{because they had hoped}* To find water there, and could not. Or; *because they had trusted:* had relied on those brooks, and made sure account to quench their thirst there. Heb. *because he had hoped*; That is, every one of the passengers hoped for a draught. Or, *because it had hoped*; that is, their heart.

*{they came thither}* Heb. *to it.* To the place where they had seen water before, and hoped to find it now. For the Hebrew particle agrees not in gender with נחל, *a brook*; but with מקום, *a place*; which is feminine, Job 20. 9. And the place is mentioned vers. 17. as remaining when the water was gone. For *Came*, see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{and were ashamed}* So it is well translated, because it is joined with *confounded*, in the same verse, as also Psal. 71. 24. yet sometimes it signifies to dig, Gen. 26. 19, 21. And then the sense is, that finding no water in the brooks, they were forced to dig wells by the way, else they and their cattle might have perished. See the like, Exod. 7. 24. Psal. 84. 6.

**V. 21. ***{For now ye are nothing; ye see my casting-down, and are afraid}* In this verse is laid down the application of the former similitude.

*{For}* He gives a reason, why he charged them with deceitfulness ver. 15. and it is fitly brought in after the similitude, that it might sink the deeper into them. So Nathan dealt with David, 2 Sam. 12. 1, 7.

*{now}* In this time of my adversity. You seemed friends in prosperity, but now you fail me.

*{ye are nothing}* Heb. *not.* To me. You are not little, but nothing at all to me, Job 13. 4. You afford me no comfort at all in my misery. For all your great show of kindness, it had been as good for me you had not come, for any good I have gotten by you. Or, as the Hebrew Bibles advise us in the margin to read, *ye are like to it*; that is, to the brook forementioned. You afford me no more streams of comfort, than it did of water to the thirsty travelers: See the like various reading in the notes, Psal. 100. 3. Job 13. 15. Isa. 63. 9.

*{ye see my, &c.}* You think God has cast me down for my wickedness. For *See*, look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{casting down}* Affliction so called, because it dejects a man's spirits, makes his body weaker, and his estate poorer. All this it did to Job.

*{and are afraid}* You are afraid to come nigh me, Psal. 38. 11. & 88. 18. lest I should infect you, or the like trouble should befall you for keeping company with me. Or, you are afraid to speak the truth of my condition, and judge me to be a wicked man, because I am thus plagued, and therefore will afford me no comfort in my extremity.

**V. 22. ***{Did I say, Bring unto me? or give a reward for me of your substance?}* If I had looked for relief from you, or money to redeem me from my miseries, whereby to put you to expenses, you might have had some occasion to censure me thus. But all I looked for, was comfort, which you seemed to offer to me, by your voluntary coming, (for I sent not for you) yet you have added to my sorrows.

*{Did I say}* Or, *Is it because I said?* Did any speech of mine occasion so hard a censure of me? You did it without any provocation, and therefore your sin is the greater. For *Say*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Bring unto me}* Heb. *give unto me*, Prov. 30. 15. Hos. 4. 18. How would you have helped me with your money, that deny me good words?

*{a reward for me}* Or, *gift*, or, *bribe.* To free me from my troubles, if they may be bought out; or to ransom me from the enemy, ver. 23.

*{of your substance}* The word sometimes signifies *strength*, but here *riches*, which is a man's civil strength, Prov. 18. 11. So the word is used, Prov. 5. 10. For *Substance*, see on v. 11. on *Strength.*

**V. 23. ***{Or deliver me from the enemies hand, or redeem me from the hand of the mighty?}* Did I make any motion of cost to you at all?

*{deliver me}* Set me free.

*{from the enemies}* It comes from a word that signifies to besiege, or straiten; for enemies bring men into great straits, Neh. 9. 27.

*{hand}* From the power of the enemy, Chap. 1. 12. For *Hand*, see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{redeem me}* By price, or force.

*{of the mighty}* Or, *terrible ones.* For they that have strength, are terrible to others, and fright them with their great power.

**V. 24. ***{Teach me, and I will hold my tongue; and cause me to understand wherein I have erred}* Though I think I have justly defended myself all this while, yet you shall see, that I am not indocible, if you will show me my error.

*{Teach me}* The word ירה signifies to cast a dart, or stone, and to dart the rain from the clouds; and lastly, to teach, which distills knowledge from heaven into men's hearts, as rain does moisture into the earth, Ezek. 20. 46. & 21. 2. Deut. 32. 2. Heb. 6. 7, 8. Hence the law and doctrine have their derivation in the original, and are called תורה, Psal. 1. 2. Thence a teacher has his name מורה.

*{and I will hold my tongue}* The word signifies to plow, and thence to meditate, or invent, to search out things hidden, as the plow does; and lastly, to be silent, because musing men speak least. I will complain no more of my sorrows, but hear you quietly; and not contradict you, but learn of you. Or, I will by my silence confess my former error, when I am better informed, Chap. 40. 4, 5. Prov. 30. 32. For *Holding the tongue*, see on Chap. 1. 14. on *Plowing.*

*{cause me to understand}* Convince me by arguments. Think not to beat me down with reproaches and threats, but overcome me with solid reasons.

*{wherein I have erred}* The word signifies bodily and local errors, and wanderings, Ezek. 34. 6. and by a figure, the errors of the mind, Prov. 5. 19, 20. but especially such as arise out of ignorance, not out of willfulness, Lev. 4. 2. & 5. 18. & 22. 14. Psal. 19. 12. Job means here his ignorance in judgment, or in carriage under his afflictions.

**V. 25. ***{How forcible are right words? but what doth your arguing reprove?}* I confess there is much force in solid reasons, neither could I have contradicted you all this while; but what advantage has your censorious discourse gotten of me?

*{How forcible}* He speaks by way of admiration, showing that he cannot express the force of strong arguments, how persuasive they are. See the like phrase, Psal. 31. 19. & 84. 1.

*{right words}* Sound and solid arguments brought to prove the truth.

*{but what}* Your words have no such solidity in them. They prove nothing at all; at least not what they were brought to prove. They are vain, and cannot convince me.

*{doth your arguing reprove}* Arguing contains both replying, and answering, Chap. 13. 3. your words are so weak, that you can neither make good your own assertions, nor overthrow mine. For *Arguing*, see on Chap. 13. 3. on *Reason.*

**V. 26. ***{Do ye imagine to reprove words, and the speeches of one that is desperate; which are as wind?}* In this and the next verse, Job expostulates with them about their former words. At the 28. verse he advises them to more moderation for time to come.

*{Do ye imagine to reprove words?}* Think you that I have spoken nothing but words, without weight, which you may easily confute? Or, *Do ye think that words reprove?* Do you think to confute me with bare words, without any reason in them. So it is fitly knit with the words foregoing. You have got nothing of me, for empty words cannot convince me; yet you suppose that your words are all sentences, and invincible arguments against me, because you are in prosperity.

*{and the speeches}* Think you that you have no solid reasons from me to answer, but only desperate words?

*{of one desperate}* That thinks his miserable state to be incurable, Jer. 2. 25. He means himself, who by reason of the weight of his misery, had no hope of delivery.

*{which are as wind}* Or, *And that the words of one desperate are wind?* Because my state is hopeless, think you that my words are brainless? Chap. 15. 2. & 16. 3. Eccl. 5. 16. Jer. 5. 13. Hos. 12. 1. Prov. 11. 29. Eph. 4. 14. Endeavors, without success, and words without substance, are in the places quoted compared to wind. For *Wind*, see Chap. 15. 2. on *Vaine.*

**V. 27. ***{Yea ye overwhelm the fatherless, and you dig a pit for your friend}* He shows their open cruelty, by seeking to undo one helpless like a fatherless child, and their secret plots to effect it, by digging a pit, not for an enemy, but for a friend, that might expect better usage from them.

*{ye overwhelm}* Heb. *ye make to fall upon.* You make gins to fall on me, as men make traps to fall on vermin, which kill them with their weight. Or, you throw yourselves on me with all your strength, that you may be sure to hold me fast till you have destroyed me, as hunters and wild beasts fall on their prey.

*{the fatherless}* Me, whom, being like a fatherless child, you should defend; Chap. 22. 9. & 24. 3, 9. Psal. 94. 6. Lam. 5. 3. So he meant himself, by *one desperate*, vers. 26. It is not enough that you comfort me not, as I thought you would have done, who are so wise, and came so readily without calling, but you fall violently on me, an helpless man, with reproaches.

*{and dig a pit}* As men dig pits to catch wild beasts, so you frame false arguments to catch me, and drive me into the pit of despair, by charging me with hypocrisy. For the phrase, see Psal. 7. 15. & 64. 5. Jer. 18. 20. Or, *And make a feast on*, &c. You are not content to destroy me, but you feed on me also, and devour me, and insult over me in the midst of my miseries, Chap. 41. 6. 2 Kings 6. 23. So the Philistines dealt with Samson, Judg. 16. 23.

*{for your friend}* For me, whom you profess much friendship to, and who ever loved you, and never deserved any such dealing from you.

**V. 28. ***{Now therefore be content, look upon me, for it is evident unto you, if I lie}* Though you have been so harsh to me hitherto, yet now at length be kinder to me. Deal more calmly with me.

*{be content, look upon me}* See the like, vers. 19. View me well, as a judge does a malefactor, you shall see I can endure it without blushing, because I am not guilty of what you accuse me, Psal. 11. 4. Rev. 6. 16. Prov. 20. 8. Consider my cause, and you will find that I speak as one overladen with sorrow, and am not an hypocrite. Or, *Be well pleased to look on me.* So the latter of two verbs of the same tense is translated in the infinitive, 1 Sam. 2. 3. Eccl. 8. 3. And the meaning is, Cast a loving countenance upon me, and be not so hot, and so averse as before. Turn not your eyes away from me, as men do from one whom they will not vouchsafe to look upon, or cannot endure to behold, Psal. 10. 1. & 44. 24. Isa. 53. 3. Or, *Be content to turn to me.* So the word is used, Chap. 5. 1. and it suits well with the following verse. Think better of me than you did before. Or, Attend well to what I say, for I will declare my mind plainly. For *Content*, see on v. 9. on *Please.*

*{it is evident unto you}* Heb. *before your face.* It will quickly appear by that which I have said, or shall say, that I speak truth, if you be not prepossessed with prejudice against me because of my troubles. Or, *to your face.* I should be impudent, if I should speak falsely before you. See on Chap. 1. 11. For *Face*, see on Chap. 14. 20. on *Countenance.*

*{if I lie}* If I speak falsely, or maintain a bad cause to favor myself, you will easily find it. Or, the matter lies plain before you; if I lie, let God plague me. See on Chap. 1. 11.

**V. 29. ***{Return I pray you, let it not be iniquity, yea, return again; my righteousness is in it}* He goes on to persuade his friends to a more careful and kind attention for the future, or a more candid interpretation of his former words.

*{Return}* The word signifies literally a local return, metaphorically a return from anger, or change of resolution; so here, Return from your cruel speeches against me. Or, weigh the matter better, look it over again more seriously, and you will see your error, and my righteous cause, Chap. 17. 10. For *Return*, see on Chap. 14. 13. on *Past.*

*{I pray you}* See on Chap. 4. 7.

*{let it not be iniquity}* Object not iniquity against me, as before. I may be weak, but am not wicked; as follows, *my righteousnesse is in it.* Or, Deal not so unjustly with me in your after-speeches, as before, lest God lay iniquity to your charge.

*{yea, return again}* He repeats it again, to show the weight of the matter, and necessity of hearkening to it, Cant. 6. 13. Phil. 4. 4.

*{my righteousness is in it}* I am righteous in this matter, Chap. 19. 28. Or, my righteousness is, or shall be made plain in my defense, as vers. 30. Consider of the same things again and again, you will come to this conclusion at last, to wit, that I am no hypocrite.

**V. 30. ***{Is there iniquity in my tongue? Cannot my taste discern perverse things?}* In this last verse, Job clears his former speeches from untruth, by a similitude, taken from the palate. And so prepares his friends to hear further with patience, what he had to say in the next chapter.

*{Is there iniquity in my tongue?}* Can you show any unjust or untrue thing in all that I have said? For *Tongue*, see on Chap. 15. 5.

*{Cannot?}* Certainly it can.

*{my taste}* Heb. *my palate*, The instrument of tasting, and it is exquisite therein.

*{discern}* Heb. *understand.* That is, perceive a difference between sound food, and unsound. So understanding is attributed to the tongue, Psal. 52. 2. To the mouth, Josh. 1. 8. To the hands, Psal. 78. 72. To the ear, Job 12. 11. & 34. 3. To pots, Psal. 58. 9. *Before your pots can feel* Heb. *understand.*

*{perverse things}* Harsh meats, such as are unpleasing to the palate, and unfit for nourishment. All this is spoken metaphorically; and Job's meaning is, that the soul has her taste, as well as the body, that is, her reason, whereby she discerns truth from falsehood, as the palate does wholesome and savory meat, from unwholesome and unsavory: And he affirms, that his spiritual palate has skill herein: and that he could perceive that they had spoken perversely and falsely of him, and abused good grounds to condemn him. Or, They should see, that he could avoid false speeches, and knew how to speak right things, and had, and would speak accordingly. For *Perverse things*, see on v. 2. on *Calamity.*

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1. ***{Is there not an appointed time to man upon the earth? are not his days also like the days of an hireling?}* Some think Job confutes that which Eliphaz contended to prove, that he was punished for his wickedness, by showing that affliction is common to all men. Others, that he confutes that promise, Chap. 5. 20. by showing, that men cannot look for uninterrupted happiness here. Others, that he confirms what Eliphaz had said of man's troubles, and seeks to God, as he directed him, that they might see that he was not unteachable; as Chap. 6. 24. But the right coherence is, that having confuted Eliphaz in the former chapter, in this he confirms his desire to die; by arguments taken from the condition of man in general; vers. 1. of some men in special, vers. 2. of himself in particular, vers. 3. and so on to the seventh.

*{an appointed time}* Or, *a warfare.* The word צבא signifies, first, warfare, as 1 Sam. 28. 1. Isa. 40. 2. from thence it is used for an host, or army, Nehem. 9. 6. Zeph. 1. 5. and then for an appointed time; because there was a set time for soldiers to fight, and a set time also for them to serve. Therefore the Levites are compared to an host, and must begin their service at thirty years of age, and end it at fifty, Num. 4. 3. So we have a set time here to grapple with miseries. Thus the word signifies, Chap. 14. 14. Seeing I have a set time of abiding here, why may I not enjoy that time, but am deprived of the comfort of it?

*{to man}* Every man's time is set, and his days numbered, Chap. 14. 4. yet he has an eye more particularly to himself; as Chap. 3. 3. Psal. 39. 11. & 109. 16. The word signifies sorrowful, or miserable man. His sorrows end not, till his life end, ver. 17. Chap. 9. 17.

*{upon the earth}* To live upon the face of the earth. For as for his condition in heaven or hell, there is no end of it. For *Earth*, see on Chap. 9. 24.

*{his}* Every man's, masters', and servants'; great men's, and mean men's.

*{days}* He reckons by days, not by years, to point out the shortness of man's life. For *Day*, see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{like the days of an hireling}* Hirelings were sometimes hired for years, as Isai. 16. 14. & 21. 16. but ordinarily by days, Job 14. 6. Matth. 20. 1, 2. and at night their labor ends. Man's days of his life are certainly determined, and short like an hireling's. Therefore I may well wish for an end of mine. If an hireling have a time set for his painful service, and look for it daily, may not I wish for death, the end of my troubles?

**V. 2. ***{As a servant earnestly desireth the shadow, and as an hireling looketh for the reward of his work.}* Job farther sets out his desire of death, by the servants' and hirelings' desire of refreshing, and end of their labors, that they might have rest, and reward.

*{a servant}* The word properly signifies one taken in wars, or bought with money, yet it is extended to all kind of household servants.

*{earnestly desireth}* Or, *that earnestly desireth.* So the Chald. paraphrase reads it. Heb. *gapeth after, or gaspeth for.* Which is more emphatical. Like a man gasping for life. It argues a very great desire of a thing. So the word is used, Chap. 36. 20.

*{the shadow}* In those hot countries, the heat of the sun was very burdensome to day-laborers in the fields, which made them very desirous of shady places to work in, or rest under at noon. A thing usual in harvest time. But it is more likely, that Job means the shadow of the night, the time when his life should end, as the servant's work does for that day, Chap. 36. 20. when the shadows declining, or extending themselves, give warning of the setting of the sun, to the joy of the servants, who long for the end of their day's work. See on Psal. 102. 11. & 109. 23. Or, by the shadow, may be meant the house, where the sun shines not, as abroad, and which is a shelter from heat and storms, Gen. 19. 8. Psal. 17. 8. & 27. 5. and 31. 20. & 19. 1. And then the sense is, As a servant that works in the hot sun without, desires that the night would come, that he might go into the house to rest; so have I reason to desire death, to be eased of my pain, Isai. 4. 6. & 16. 3. and 25. 4, 5. & 32. 2. Psal. 57. 1.

*{an hireling}* See on ver. 1.

*{looketh for}* With his eyes still towards it, as a thing much desired, Psal. 25. 15. & 123. 1, 2.

*{the reward of his work}* As desire of rest makes the servant long for night, so desire of reward moves the hireling to wish the day were done: for they were daily to be paid, Lev. 19. 13. Deut. 24. 14, 15. The work, in Hebrew, is put often for the reward, for they should not be parted, Jam. 5. 4. See Levit. 19. 13. Psal. 109. 20. Isai. 40. 10. & 49. 4. So the petition is put for the thing prayed for, 1 Sam. 1. 27. And sin, for the punishment of sin, Gen. 4. 13. Deut. 24. 15. No hireling longs more that night were come, that he might receive his reward, than I do for death, that I might be eased of my troubles.

**V. 3. ***{So am I made to possess months of vanity, and wearisome nights are appointed to me}* The servant if he work some days, may rest others, but I have months without rest. If he work every day, yet he rests every night, but my troubles last day and night. I am worse than a servant, no marvel then if I desire an end of my life.

*{So}* A comparison, not in quantity, but in quality. There was some resemblance between them; but Job's state was worse than a servant's or hireling's. The comparison may be made full, thus; as servants and hirelings, because of their painful labors in the day, desire the night were come, so do I desire death, that I might have an end of my months of vanity, and weary nights.

*{am I made to possess}* Heb. *am I made to inherit for my selfe.* Some take it as spoken of all men in general, who receive a troublesome condition from their parents, Chap. 5. 7. and it remains with them, as inheritances do. But Job rather speaks of himself, as if he had said; I have had more vain months than others, as if vanity had been mine inheritance. He may intimate also, that his troubles came from God, who gives men inheritances.

*{months of vanity}* I have had pleasant months, but they are gone, and vain ones come in their room, Chap. 29. 2. He instances in months, and not in days, because his troubles were lasting troubles. And he calls them vain or empty months, because they were empty of comfort; like the dull winter months, that afford no light to cheer men's spirits, or fruit to refresh them, Gal. 2. 21. Or, they afford not that comfort I looked for, and so made my expectation vain, as the Israelites' hope was, when they were slain in the wilderness, and not permitted to enter into Canaan, Psal. 78. 33, 34.

*{and wearisome nights}* My days which should be spent in profitable labor, are spent in vain, and the nights that should be times of rest to me, afford nothing but weariness and disquietness, so that I cannot sleep. In months of vanity, a man might rest in the nights; but Job did not, or only so little as might keep him alive to bear misery. Strong men labor in the day, Psal. 104. 22, 23. Sick men have most pain in the night, Psal. 6. 6. & 77. 2.

*{are appointed to me}* The word is used commonly for numbering, but here it signifies appointing by God; yet so exactly, as if he had numbered them to a day. So Dan. 1. 10. Or, *are prepared for me*; as the word is translated, Jon. 1. 17. In the original it is, *They have appointed wearisome nights for me.* But it is to be understood impersonally, as it is translated: Such nights are appointed to me.

**V. 4. ***{When I lie down, I say, When shall I arise, and the night be gone? and I am full of tossings to and fro, unto the dawning of the day}* An exact description of a wearisome night, when one wishes for day, as soon as he is laid in bed, and tosses till morning, and sleeps not at all in the night.

*{when}* Heb. *if.* But here it notes not only any condition, but the time of lying down. So it does, Gen. 38. 9.

*{I lie down}* In my bed, in the evening to take rest and sleep. For it appears by the words following, that it is not meant of sleep in the daytime. When others slept sweetly in their beds, I lay awake. For *Lie down*, see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{I say}* In my heart: I think with myself, Psal. 39. 1. For *Say*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{When shall I arise}* When will it be day, that I may arise? For there is no comfort in rising in the night, neither do men use to leave their beds till the morning. For *Rise*, see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{and the night}* Heb. *the evening.* From ערב, because it is mixed of light and darkness. We call it twilight: but here it is put for the whole night, whereof it is but the beginning, for Job wished the whole night past, that he might rise at the dawning of the day. The word ערב signifies. 1. A mixture or mingled people; Jer. 50. 37. 2. Arabia, 1 King. 10. 15. 3. The evening, wherein darkness and light are mingled, 1 King. 22. 35. So here.

*{be gone}* Heb. *be measured out.* All the hours of it be gone. He speaks like a sick man, that when he cannot sleep for pain, tells the clock all night, and wishes the last hour past. Or; *he measure out the night.* That is, God; who metes out the hours of day and night, which they cannot pass. Or, *it*: that is, Job's heart, which all this while was wishing for the morning.

*{I am full}* The word signifies satisfaction in the original. I am as full of tossings, as a man is of meat; when he has filled his belly: so full of confusion, Chap. 10. 15. & 14. 1. Psal. 88. 3. & 123. 3. Prov. 1. 31.

*{of tossings}* From one side of the bed to the other. So sick men use to do in hope to find ease, yet find it nowhere.

*{unto the dawning of the day}* All night long. So the word is used for the beginning of light in the morning, 1 Sam. 30. 17. Psal. 119. 147. Though more ordinarily it signifies the beginning of the evening, as 2 King. 7. 5. compared with ver. 7. Isai. 5. 11. Prov. 7. 9. which gave occasion to some, to read the latter end of the verse thus, *And when the night is gone, I am full of tossings to and fro, untill the twilight*; to wit, of the evening. I take no rest by night, nor by day: See the like, Deut. 28. 67. The word נשף signifies: 1. Darkness, Jer. 13. 16. 2. The twilight of morning, or evening, or dawning, Prov. 7. 9. So here.

**V. 5. ***{My flesh is clothed with worms and clods of dust; my skin is broken, and become loathsome}* If you consider what a case I am in, and look well upon my body, you will think I complain not without a cause.

*{my flesh}* My body, whereof the flesh is a principal part. For *Flesh*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{is clothed}* Covered all over as with a garment, Psal. 104. 2. & 93. 1. & 109. 29. & 35. 26. Job 29. 14. Ezek. 7. 27.

*{with worms}* Which breed in his sores, Chap. 21. 26. Isai. 14. 11.

*{and clods of dust}* Scurf that came off from his sores by scraping them, or dust contracted by sitting on the ground, which was like clods of earth, Chap. 2. 8. A strange disease, yet a fit dress for a dying man to be full of worms and dust, like a garment covering his whole body, which was finely clad before. For *Dust*, see on Chap. 14. 8. on *Ground.*

*{my skin is broken}* Or, *cleft.* So the word is used for cleaving the sea, Isai. 51. 15. Jer. 31. 35. With ulcers, boils, and running scabs, holes are made in my skin, and it is as it were, cleft asunder.

*{and become loathsome}* Or, *melted*, Psal. 58. 7. By reason of stinking matter that ran out of his sores, which made his body yield a noisome savor to himself and others, and consume away.

**V. 6. ***{My days are swifter then a weaver's shuttle, and are spent without hope}* He mixes his desire of death, with complaints of the troubles and shortness of his life. Why should I desire to live, seeing my days are troublesome, and likely to be but short at the best, Chap. 14. 1. For *Dayes*, see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{my days}* The days of my prosperity, for his ill days went not away so fast, but tarried too long, Chap. 3. 11. &c. Others take it for the days of his life, which passed away apace. See on ver. 1. Chap. 16. 22. Psal. 90. 6. & 102. 11. & 103. 15. & 144. 4. Isai. 40. 6. Jam. 4. 14.

*{swifter}* It comes from a word that signifies light, because light things are swift, and heavy things slow. So we say, light of foot, that is, swift. Hab. 1. 8. Job 9. 25. Isai. 5. 26. & 18. 2. Jer. 2. 23.

*{then a weaver's shuttle}* Which passes suddenly from one end of the web to the other, and is used proverbially to set out swiftness. The Heb. word ארג signifies. 1. A weaver's shuttle. As here. 2. The web, Judg. 16. 14.

*{are spent}* The word sometimes signifies finishing, or consummating a thing, as Gen. 2. 1. but here it imports a consumption, or ending, as Jer. 14. 12. Exod. 33. 3. For *Spend*, see on Chap. 36. 11.

*{without hope}* Heb. *in not hope.* See the like phrase, Prov. 14. 28. Dan. 8. 25. without hope of recovering of my prosperity, or of returning to life again, as ver. 7. He intimates, that he had no hope of those things which Eliphaz foretold him, Chap. 5. 24. for his sores would soon kill him.

**V. 7. ***{O remember that my life is wind: mine eye shall no more see good}* He turns his speech now, from his friends, to God, as Jacob did from his sons, Gen. 49. 18. He speaks not to Eliphaz, as before, but to God the author of life and death, as appears by his petition, which none else could grant, to wit, the abating of his sorrows in regard of the shortness of his life.

*{Remember}* God cannot forget; but he is said to remember a thing, when he does something, that may make men to see, that he takes notice of it. So God remembered his covenant, Exod. 2. 24. when he sent Moses to deliver his people, Exod. 3. 8. So Psal. 74. 2, 3. & 78. 38, 39. & 137. 7. Gen. 8. 1. So here. You seem, O Lord, to forget my frail condition, by laying more load on me, than I can well bear. Show that you remember me, by easing me; and deal with me, as with a weak short-lived creature.

*{my life is wind}* The word signifies a spirit, and the wind; because it is like a spirit, invisible and powerful. He means that his life was like a wind, speedily passing away, and not returning; as Psal. 78. 39. Chap. 8. 9. & 14. 2. 1 Pet. 1. 24. The note of similitude is here omitted, to make the sentence the more emphatical; as ver. 12. & Chap. 6. 12. Prov. 11. 22. The Chaldee paraphrase reads it, *My life is as wind.* For *Wind*, see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Vaine.*

*{mine eye}* I myself shall no more, with mine eyes, behold good, Chap. 19. 27. For *Eye*, see on Chap. 2. 12.

*{shall no more see}* Heb. *shall not return to see.* Seeing, is not here taken for looking on a thing, that a man has nothing to do withal; but for enjoying it, or, having a part in it, Psal. 16. 10, 11. & 128. 5, 6. Chap. 20. 17. & 21. 20. Lam. 3. 1. Jer. 17. 6. Psal. 88. 48. & 27. 13. Chap. 9. 25. & 33. 28. Joh. 3. 36. 1 Pet. 3. 10. Psal. 34. 12. for the eye brings much comfort both here, and in heaven, Isa. 38. 11. Psal. 27. 4. Mat. 5. 8. For *Return*, see on Chap. 14. 13. on *Past.* For *See*, look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{good}* Worldly prosperity (as Psal. 4. 6.) which I have had so great a share in; for he made account to see God in heaven, Chap. 19. 27. I shall no more return to my former prosperity; or, when I am dead, I shall not return hither any more to my possessions. For *Good*, see on Chap. 21. 13. on *wealth.*

**V. 8. ***{The eye of him that hath seen me, shall see me no more: thine eyes are upon me, and I am not}* In the former verse he had excluded his own eye from sight of good: in this verse he excludes others from sight of him. I shall have no comfort in seeing men, nor honor from them.

*{the eye}* That is, the man that has eyes. No man can fasten his eyes upon me. For seeing is the action of the man, though performed by the eye. See on ver. 7. For *Seene*, look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{of him that hath seen me}* Heb. *that seeth me.* As the word is translated, Gen. 16. 13. That looks upon me now. None of mine acquaintance shall see me any more. Or, *of sight*; meaning the quickest sighted eye.

*{shall see me no more}* A description of death, which takes men away from the sight of their friends and neighbors, Chap. 20. 9. And as here it is set out by others not seeing us; so elsewhere, by our not seeing others, Isa. 38. 11. Death takes away all controversy here between man and man. The Heb. word שור signifies. 1. To sing: As in the title of Psal. 7. 1. 2. To see, or behold, Numb. 24. 17. 3. To go, Isa. 57. 9. Here it is taken in the second sense for seeing.

*{thine eyes are upon me}* As soon as you set your eyes upon me to destroy me, I shall be dead. See the like phrase, Psal. 104. 29. I cannot endure your flaming eyes sparkling forth wrath against me. See Chap. 16. 9. Psal. 17. 11, 12. Amos 9. 4. 1 Sam. 18. 9. where setting the eyes upon one is for evil, though elsewhere it be for good; as Gen. 44. 21. Jer. 39. 12. & 40. 4. & 24. 6. Or, though I see not worldly prosperity, when I am in the grave; nor others see me, yet you will see me there. Or you may seek me then to do me good; but it will be too late, when I am dead, which will be shortly, if these afflictions be not quickly taken away. See ver. 21. The first interpretation is the best, and most suitable to the text.

*{and I am not}* I can live no longer in this world; I shall not be alive here. See the like phrase, ver. 21. Gen. 5. 24. & 42. 13. with 44. 20. Psal. 103. 16. Jer. 31. 15. Matth. 2. 18. See on Job 3. 16.

**V. 9. ***{As the cloud is consumed and vanisheth away; so he that goeth down to the grave, shall come up no more}* Before he had compared his life to a passing wind, ver. 7. now to a vanishing cloud; and all to move God to pity him.

*{As}* This word is not in the original; but is well added; because the words following show, that it is brought for a similitude. See the like, Prov. 11. 22. Mic. 3. 12. Psal. 119. 119. Deut. 28. 23. Yet sometimes this note of similitude is not added in the translation. See on ver. 7.

*{the cloud}* Which is made of vapors arising from the earth, and hangs in the air by the power of God. This similitude is brought, not to show how suddenly man's life may be taken away, (which yet is true, and may fitly be shadowed out by the vanishing of a cloud) as it is, Chap. 30. 15. but to set out the irrecoverableness of our life here, when it is once gone; as appears by that which goes before, ver. 7, 8. and by that which follows in v. 10. The Heb. word ענן is used. 1. To set out a cloud, 1 King. 8. 10. 2. Smoke, Lev. 16. 13. 3. Calamity, Ezek. 34. 12. 4. Multitude, Jer. 4. 13. Here in the first sense.

*{is consumed}* Scattered by the wind, or dried up by the heat of the sun. Or, *is spent*; as v. 6. it is emptied and dissolved into rain. For *Consumed*, see on Chap. 36. 11. on *Spend.*

*{vanisheth away}* Is clean gone out of sight; no footsteps or memorial of it remaining, Jam. 4. 14. So our sins vanish out of God's remembrance, Isa. 44. 22. Heb. *goeth.* Not to some other place; but being turned into air or water, goes clean away out of sight; so man, by death, goes out of this world, and out of the sight of men, v. 8. A metaphor from living creatures, that remove from place to place. See on Chap. 1. 19. The word הלך signifies. 1. To go about a thing. Chap. 14. 2. 2. To walk, Gen. 35. 19. 3. The passage of ships, Psal. 104. 26. 4. Passing out of the world, or dying, Chap. 14. 20. 5. Constancy in a condition, Gen. 15. 2. 6. A course of life, Psal. 1. 1. 7. Vanishing or not appearing, Psal. 58. 8. So here.

*{He that goes down}* A proper description of burial; for the grave is in the bowels of the earth. So Gen. 37. 35. & 42. 38. Ezek. 26. 20. See how it is called ascending, Chap. 5. 26.

*{to the grave}* The word in the original comes from שאל to ask or desire. For the grave is never satisfied; but always desiring, Prov. 30. 16. שאול is taken variously in Scripture: as first, for hell, Prov. 15. 11. Psal. 9. 17. Secondly, for miseries that seem to us irrecoverable, Psal. 86. 13. Jon. 2. 2. Psal. 30. 3. Thirdly, for the lower parts of the earth, Psal. 139. 8. Fourthly, for the state of the dead, Isa. 38. 18. Gen. 37. 35. For Jacob did not think that Joseph was buried; but devoured by a wild beast. Fifthly, for the grave; as Prov. 30. 16. So here; but, as the grave in general, is considered, as common to all men; for each man's particular grave is called קבר.

*{shall come up no more}* Into this world, to live among men, or re-enjoy his house and goods he had before; as ver. 10. Chap. 16. 22. Psal. 39. 13. 2 Sam. 14. 14. Isa. 38. 11. for it appears, that Job denied not the resurrection, (as some of the Jewish Rabbins gather out of this text) by what he professes, Chap. 14. 12. & 19. 26, 27. Lazarus by a miracle was raised up to live again among men for a while; but that was extraordinary, and he is long since dead again, and must abide so till the resurrection.

**V. 10. ***{He shall return no more to his house: neither shall his place know him any more}* He shall have no more to do with worldly labors, or domestical comforts.

*{return no more to his house}* To dwell there where he did before, or to order his domestical affairs. The same cloud returns no more; though others come in the room, Eccl. 12. 2. so the same men return not into the world after death; but their children supply their room. For *Return*, see on Chap. 14. 13. on *Past.* For *House*, on Chap. 15. 28.

*{his place know him any more}* Some take it for his office and dignity; as Prov. 25. 6. Eccl. 9. 16. If he were a great man in his lifetime, now his office is clean gone: another enjoys it, Psal. 109. 8. Others, for the men of the place; as the house before, for his children and servants: Others, the place here for his friends and neighbors, Chap. 20. 9. Gen. 18. 26. & 20. 11. so the city is put for the men of the city, Gen. 18. 28. & 19. 15. Neither his family nor his friends shall take any further notice of him; but as clean forget him in a little time, as if they had never known him. But neither of these seem to be the sense of the place. Not the former; because it is joined to the house, which is nothing to his office. Not the latter; because it is intimated before, ver. 8. And this seems to be an higher expression of the forgotten estate of the dead. His house and place are but a metaphor set out as living creatures, that were wont, as it were, to welcome him home, and receive him cheerfully after travels, or business abroad, when he returned; but now would not own him, if he could return. See the like metaphor, Chap. 1. 19. and the phrase, Psal. 103. 16. The sum of all, is, that within a little time after his death, he should be utterly forgotten.

**V. 11. ***{Therefore, I will not refrain my mouth, I will speak in the anguish of my spirit, I will complain in the bitterness of my soul}* Because I must shortly die, and then I and my miseries shall both be forgotten, and I deprived of all liberty of complaining; therefore I will satisfy my spirit with complaining now: I will do it abundantly to ease myself. Men in trouble think it some refreshment to have liberty to complain. For grief kept in, like fire, burns the more.

*{Therefore}* Heb. *Also.* Seeing you will not leave punishing me, I will not leave complaining of your harsh dealing with me. For *Therefore*, see on Chap. 16. 4. on *Also.*

*{I will not refrain my mouth}* Heb. *I will not prohibit my mouth.* I will not stop it, nor hinder it from speaking. I will not suppress my grief in silence, nor my sighs, which would kill me, if I should not utter them. For grief strives in a man to get utterance, and to pour out itself in words. I will not spare words, but let all come out. So the word is used, Isa. 58. ver. 1. For *Mouth*, see on Chap. 15. 5.

*{in the anguish of my spirit}* The Hebrew word signifies *straitnesse.* I am straitened with keeping in my grief, and much pained, like a man girt in with a cord. I will give my sorrow a vent. What I shall speak, is not from the lips outward, but comes from a sorrowful heart. Or, *of the anguish of my spirit.* I will declare what sorrow I feel within, far more than others do imagine. So this particle is used, Esth. 3. 12. For *Spirit*, see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Vaine.*

*{I will complain}* The word signifies first to meditate, then to produce such things in words, as lay hid in the mind. Job had many sad thoughts in him, and now he meant to utter them, Psal. 142. 2. it is called, pouring out a complaint. It was first conceived in the heart, and then poured out of the lips, 1 Sam. 1. 15, 16.

*{in}* Or, *because of.* So it is used, Neh. 5. 3. Or, *of*; as before.

*{the bitterness}* As bitter things are displeasing to the palate; so are troubles to the spirit; and therefore heaviness of heart, is often in Scripture, set out by bitterness, Ruth 1. 20. Exod. 1. 14. See on Job 3. 20.

*{of my soul}* The pains were in his body, but the grief in his soul; and that he complains more of, than of bodily pains. For *Soul*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

**V. 12. ***{Am I a sea, or a whale, that thou settest a watch over me?}* Here begins his new complaint; mentioned ver. 11.

*{Am I}* Tell me whether I be so or no. Too bold a speech to God from a creature. See the like, Chap. 6. 12. He turns his speech to God, and in his impatience, lays all his troubles upon him.

*{a sea}* As a sea. See on v. 7. Am I as unruly as a sea, that is unruly, and can hardly be kept quiet, Psal. 65. 7. & 93. 3. Or rather, Am I as strong as the sea, that can bear all winds and storms, and yet subsist? For *Sea*, see on Chap. 14. 11.

*{or a whale}* Can I bear all troubles, as the sea receives all waters, and the whale bears all tempests? The word תנין signifies any great or terrible creature. Sometimes it is put for some great land creature, as dragons, Ezek. 29. 3. Jer. 9. 11. & 51. 37. Sometimes for some great fish in the sea, supposed to be the whale; as Gen. 1. 21. and so here, because it is joined with the sea; as Psal. 74. 13.

*{that thou settest}* Or, *That thou shouldst set.* What need is there of such a guard?

*{a watch over me}* Prisons are fitter for wild beasts of the earth, than for fishes of the sea; yet the sea is imprisoned by the land, Psal. 104. 9. Jer. 5. 22. Job 38. 8, 10. and the whales are shut up in the sea. Do you think that I would do mischief, if I were not kept in by violence, as the sea and whale would, if they were let loose? My former carriage in my prosperity shows the contrary. So some interpret it, but Job intended not a keeping of him in from doing hurt; for the whale is not so kept in, but it may hurt the lesser fishes. The thing that Job intended was, that he was kept in his troubles by God's mighty power, as a prisoner by a watch or guard, that he could no way escape. So the sea is kept within its bounds, and the whale surrounded, when they go to take him. So good men set a watch over their lips, that no ill word escape them, Psal. 39. 1. & 141. 3. Thus have God's servants been so compassed about with troubles, as prisoners with walls and with a guard, that they could see no way of getting out, Psal. 18. 4, 5. & 116. 3. Jon. 2. 5, 6. Chap. 13. 27. Lam. 3. 7. Job desired death very much as an end of his troubles, Chap. 3. 21, 22. but could find no way to escape at that door.

**V. 13. ***{When I say, My bed shall comfort me, my couch shall ease my complaint}* He goes on to show the greatness of his grief, and his unquiet condition, and to amplify his sorrows, in that they gave him no intermission, or time of rest. He was full of pain in the day, yet had some hope, that the night, which afforded sweet sleep to others, would afford him some little ease also; but he could find no such favor. The usual means of refreshing, by lying in bed, or on couches failed his expectation. In the fourth verse he had showed that his restlessness in the night, made him wish for day. Here he shows, that when day was come, his pains made him wish for night again; so he had no satisfaction day nor night. He wishes for change, as princes desire to go from one stately palace to another; not that they find satisfaction where they go, but because they find it not where they were.

*{when I say}* When I think within myself, and make account of it, Psal. 39. 1. & 53. 1. Gen. 20. 11. and ver. 4. of this chapter. For *Say*, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{my bed}* The rest I shall find in my bed. My sleep. Though the day would yield me no ease, yet I was persuaded, that the night, when it came, would yield me quiet sleep.

*{shall comfort me}* Shall afford me matter of comfort. For the bed could not speak to him; yet it might ease him: as follows in this verse.

*{my couch}* Some take it for a place to rest on in the day; but it may well be all one with the bed; because, both here, and in the verse following, he speaks of sleep, which is most usual in the night. So the bed and the couch are used for the same, Psal. 6. 6. and there, with manifest relation to the night.

*{shall ease}* Heb. *shall lift up*, or *bear*, or *take away.* It shall lift up, or take away my pain from me, that lies as a burden upon me, and makes me to complain. Sleep is an ease to sorrows for the time, and the bed an usual place of sleep, and, by consequence of ease. I lie down with an heavy burden of sorrow and care, hoping to get a little breathing: Such a burden Cain had, Gen. 4. 13. For *Ease*, see on Chap. 21. 3. on *Suffer.*

*{my complaint}* Or, *Ease me in my complaint.* Repeating *Me*, out of the former part of the verse, which is not unusual in the Scripture. Or, *Give ease in my complaint*; Or, *To my complaint.* So this particle is used, Ezra 9. 7. To the sword, &c. Or, *Ease of my complaint*; that is, take away part of it at least, if not all. So it is used, Exod. 12. 43. & 14. 28. Lev. 8. 32. & 22. 4.

**V. 14. ***{Then thou scarest me with dreams, and terrifiest me through visions}* It falls out far otherwise than I expected. I find myself not only quite disappointed of the rest I looked for; but also am scared most, where I expected most comfort.

*{Then}* Not in the day, when he said so within himself; but in the night following, when he made account of rest; as he had said, ver. 13.

*{thou}* You, O God. Satan might be the instrument of sending these dreams, as he was of taking away Job's goods; yet that also is attributed to God, Chap. 1. 21. And it was within his commission to affright Job's mind, as well as to afflict his body. Any trouble he might bring upon him, so he saved his life, Chap. 2. 6. All inferior causes are ordered by God; and to him good men look up in time of trouble; and him they accuse in passion, as Job does here, because they know none can affright or hurt them without his leave.

*{scarest me}* Cast me down to the ground with affrightments, or break me, as it were, in pieces. What sleep I have, terrifies me; but eases me not at all, rather affrights me: All my comforts prove crosses.

*{with dreams}* The original comes from a word that signifies to be thick, and to be in health. Men in health dream of those things in the night, which they are busied about in the day; and those dreams arise out of thick vapors, flying up into the head. But these dreams of Job were extraordinary, arising out of the bad humors bred in him by his sores; for sick men are most prone to dream of terrible things. It may be also Satan had an hand in it, as was noted already.

*{terrifiest me}* Represent terrible objects to me in my dreams, which scare me. Terrifying is the cause: scaring the effect. See on Chap. 6. 4.

*{through}* Or, *with.* So is this particle used, Cant. 1. 1.

*{visions}* Not revelations of heavenly mysteries; as Chap. 4. 13. but apparitions of horror. Every dream had some kind of vision in it; somewhat represented to the fantasy, Gen. 28. 12. & 37. 5, 7. & 41. 1, 2. Dan. 2. 1, 31. & 4. 5, 10. This vision might appear to him when he was awake; as Peter's did, Act. 10. 9, 11. with Act. 11. 5. but it is most likely it came when he was asleep, because it is joined to dreams. Either he could not sleep for pain, or had very unquiet sleep.

**V. 15. ***{So that}* This is inferred out of the former. I am so terrified with these night visions, that I wish myself dead.

*{my soul}* It may be taken properly for the soul; for the choice of things is in it, and the body follows the dictates of it, and cannot choose anything when the soul is gone out of it. Or it may be taken for the person; for actions are attributed to persons. So is it taken, Exod. 1. 5. For *Soul*, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{chooseth}* Not that he desired death for itself, for all things naturally desire the preservation of themselves; but he had rather be strangled, than live in that miserable condition. Not that he had any thought of strangling himself; but he intimates, that his pain was so great, that if he were put to his choice, he would choose a sudden death, rather than to remain in so troublesome a life.

*{strangling}* He shows his bitterness of spirit, in preferring a violent ignominious death before his life. Strangling was one of those kinds of death used among the Jews, for the punishment of capital offenders, such as struck father or mother, or stole an Israelite, false prophets, and such as prophesied in the name of an idol, adulterers, such as committed folly with a priest's daughter, elders who contradicted the consistory. And when the Romans overcame the Jews, they brought in crucifying instead of it, which was their manner of putting to death malefactors.

*{And death}* To be strangled till I die: or rather, any other kind of death; so that he might be gone out of the world, and be eased of his pain.

*{rather then my life}* Heb. *rather then my bones.* Rather than to abide in my bones; for the bones uphold the body, which is the house of the soul. I had rather be dead than have such rotten bones full of sores and ulcers. He mentions his bones, because his sores and pains pierced to his very bones; and he had little else left in likelihood, his flesh being consumed, and skin cracked with sores, and therefore not worth the naming. For doubtless Satan, according to the extent of his commission, had afflicted his very bones. Job had rather die, than live such an anatomy. Or the bones are put for the life; because without bones a man cannot live; for the bones uphold the body: without them no member could do his office; but the whole body would fall to the ground. Or, by a figure, the greatness of his pains is set out by the wearing of his bones; as it is, Psal. 6. 2. & 51. 8. Lam. 1. 13. Prov. 14. 30. & 17. 22. Isa. 38. 13. It goes ill with a man, when his skin is broken with running sores, worse when his flesh consumes, worst of all, when his bones are wasted. Others read it, *Death out of my bones.* That I had some deadly disease breeding in my bones, that would kill me quickly. Or that death would take my soul out from within my bones, which now keep it in, as in a prison. Others read it, *Death is desired*, or chosen *by my bones.* Repeating the word *choosing* out of the former part of the verse; and attributing desire, or choice, to the bones by a metaphor, because they would desire it, if they had understanding as the soul has. So speech is attributed to the bones, Psal. 35. 10. because they had great cause to break out into the praise of God, if they could speak. But the first reading is most proper, as best expressing the depth of Job's misery. For *Bone*, see on Chap. 2. 5.

**V. 16. ***{I loath it, I would not live alway: let me alone, for my days are vanity}* Job further amplifies the greatness of his terrors, and pains, in that they make him not only weary of his life, but also to loathe it, as a thing abominable, that he could scarce think of with patience.

*{I loath it}* I loathe my life, ver. 15. This kind of life is very tedious and noisome to me. I utterly detest it; I care not what becomes of it; I despise it. So the word is used, Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 21. Psal. 53. 5. Amos 5. 21. Or, *I loath them*; that is, his bones mentioned, v. 15.

*{I would not live alway}* In this world; for he was willing, doubtless, to live forever in heaven. Heb. *to eternity.* I were forever miserable, if I should live ever in this condition. Or, *alway*, may be taken for a long time; as *for ever* is, Psal. 132. 14. He knew that no man could live always, and therefore the word had need be so qualified. Or, it may be read, *I cannot live for ever.* Therefore spare me while I do live; let me have a little rest before I die. Or, as good die now, as at another time, for die I must ere long, and then my sorrows will end. For *Alway*, see on Chap. 22. 15. on *Old.*

*{let me alone}* Heb. *cease from me.* Give over afflicting me, Psal. 39. 13. Do not handle a man full of sores so roughly. See chap. 10. 20. & 14. 6. This is called removing of God's stroke, Psal. 39. 10. Or, cease from preserving my miserable life. Do not prolong my wretched days. I am near death; let me go quietly to my grave.

*{my days}* The days of my life. Reckoned by days, not by months or years, to show the shortness of man's life. See on ver. 1. For *Dayes*, see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{vanity}* Most vain. Like to vanity itself. See on ver. 3.

**V. 17. ***{What is man, that thou shouldst magnify him? and that thou shouldst set thine heart upon him?}* It is a very unequal match for the great God to contend with a weak man as I am.

*{what is?}* Man is nothing, not worth the looking upon. The question makes it the more emphatical; as if Job had said, I have been seriously pondering what is in man, and I find nothing in him, to make him worthy of so great esteem. See the phrase, Psal. 8. 4. & 144. 3. Heb. 2. 6. 2 Sam. 7. 18.

*{man}* Miserable, sickly, sorrowful man. A fit name for so vile a creature. See ver. 1. How little, how mean is man, how unfit to grapple with God?

*{that thou shouldst magnify him}* Not to declare his greatness; as Luk. 1. 46. Psal. 34. 3. but to prize him greatly, Rom. 11. 13. Act. 5. 13. To account him as one worthy for you to look after, or to contest with yourself, Chap. 14. 3. Or, to esteem him able to bear any afflictions by his great strength. It seems that you account so of me, in that you lay so much trouble on me. This sense the verses following that speak of trials and afflictions, do require.

*{thou shouldst set thine heart upon him}* Value him so highly, 1 Sam. 4. 20. 2 Sam. 18. 3. by accounting him as one fit to contend with you. Or, *That thou shouldst set thine heart against him.* To destroy him; as Chap. 34. 14. So God's eyes are set upon or against men to slay them, ver. 8. of this chapter. What credit is it for the great God to overcome a weak man? See the like, 1 Sam. 24. 14. Man is so vile, that he is not worthy to be looked after, or punished by you. He can do you no harm, nor is no fit enemy for you to grapple withal. It is too much honor to him for you to contend with him. Thus Job uses all kind of arguments to persuade God to mitigate his sorrows. For *Heart*, see on Chap. 15. 12.

**V. 18. ***{And that thou shouldst visit him every morning, and try him every moment?}* Here he explains what he meant in the former verse, by God's magnifying man, and setting his heart upon him; namely, thinking him a fit object for him to afflict and subdue.

*{thou shouldst visit him}* Not in mercy, as Exod. 3. 16, 17. & 4. 31. Ruth 1. 6. God's people think God neglects them when they want, and visits them, when their wants are supplied. But this visitation, here spoken of, is in anger; as appears by the word of trying, joined to it. So the word is taken, Exod. 32. 34. & 20. 5. & 34. 7. Job 35. 15. Jer. 5. 9. & 48. 44. Hos. 9. 7. what man has strength to bear such great afflictions? The word פקד signifies. 1. To visit, Exod. 20. 5. 2. To miss, 1 Sam. 25. 15. 3. To want, Isa. 34. 16. 4. To number, Numb. 7. 44. 5. To charge, or command, 2 Chron. 36. 23. 6. To set over, or make overseer, Gen. 39. 4. Here it is taken in the first sense, for visiting.

*{every morning}* Heb. *at mornings.* There are two mornings in the original; one dark and duskish, from whence it has his name in Hebrew, שחר, a little before sun-rising. The other after it, called בקר, from inquiring. For after sun-rising men begin to look after their businesses, and the morning is the fittest time to study, when men's wits are freshest. By this phrase is intimated, first the certainty of these visitations. They are as sure to come, as the sun to rise, and daylight to follow after darkness. Secondly the speediness of them: they tarry not till noon, but as good husbands take the morning light to go about their affairs, and neglect not the first occasion; so God did call Job to account, and inquire into him, as soon as he did awake and behold the daylight, Psal. 46. 5. Jer. 35. 14. See the phrase, Psal. 73. 14. Lam. 3. 23.

*{and try him}* With afflictions, to see how he can bear them, Psal. 73. 14. So God uses to try the sons of men, Jer. 9. 7. Zech. 13. 8, 9. Job 23. 10. and afflictions are called trials, especially to God's people, because they are not sent to destroy them, but to prove them, Chap. 9. 23. Ezek. 21. 13. Jam. 1. 12. 1 Pet. 1. 6, 7. Job aims at his own particular trials, although he speak of men in general. Thus you have dealt with me. As the Psalmist speaks more expressly, Psal. 73. 14. And so Job explains himself, ver. 19.

*{every moment?}* You do not only frequently, but continually afflict me. You begin in the morning to scourge me for my sins, and hold on all the day long. This is more than to do it certainly and early, which was signified by *every morning.* It is the same with *all the day long*, Psal. 44. 22. and 73. 14.

**V. 19. ***{How long wilt thou not depart from me? nor let me alone, till I swallow down my spittle?}* What he spoke of man in general, now he applies to himself in particular. Why do you lay so much load on a creature so weak as I?

*{How long}* You have given me no intermission of pain a long time. When will you do it?

*{wilt thou not depart from me?}* Look away from me, as the word is translated, Isa. 22. 4. and then take your afflicting hand off from me. How long will it be ere you give me a little interim of rest? My pain is perpetual. He speaks to God, as a man would to an enemy, to desire him to be gone, never thinking himself free from blows or hurt, while he is present. So the Gadarenes desired to be rid of Christ, Mark 5. 1, 17. He does not wish to be without God's comfortable presence, but without his afflicting presence, Psal. 39. 10. Job 14. 16. So it follows; *Let me alone.* The Heb. word שעה signifies. 1. To look on one, or respect him, Gen. 4. 4. 2. To look to one so as to trust in him, Isa. 17. 7, 8. 3. To look off one, or turn away from him, Job 14. 6. So here.

*{nor let me alone}* Not cease from supporting me, but from striking me. The word signifies to unloose a thing tied, and give it more liberty, Chap. 12. 21. Deut. 9. 14. Judg. 11. 37. How long will it be ere you loose the bands of mine affliction? Psal. 4. 1. & 116. 16. & 146. 7. See ver. 16. of this chapter.

*{till I swallow down my spittle}* Some take it to be a proverbial and hyperbolical speech intimating, that God gave him not so much time of ease, as a man may swallow his spittle in, which may be done in less than a minute. God gave him not a breathing time, Chap. 3. 24. & 9. 18. Others take it to have respect to Job's pain, whose sorrows grew upon him so fast, that they had like to choke him, and God gave him not power to spit out the corrupt matter that ran down his throat, nor time to swallow it. It certainly argues Job's continued sorrows without the least intermission.

**V. 20. ***{I have sinned, what shall I do unto thee, O thou preserver of men? why hast thou set me as a mark against thee, so that I am a burden to myself?}* Be it so, that these judgments come on me for my sins, is there no means left to pacify you? Do you observe my ways so strictly, that you pick me out from all the rest that have sinned against you, to set me before you, as a mark to shoot all your arrows in, and to afflict with so many losses, sores, and sorrows?

*{I have sinned}* Or, *Have I sinned?* as the Dutch translation reads it. *Be it so that I have committed some notorious sin, (which yet I do not grant) what would you have me to do*? Is there no way to escape your wrath, without suffering so heavy troubles? This seems to be the scope of Job's words, If I be a notorious sinner, as my companions accuse me. He does not grant himself to be so, for he stands to maintain his innocency. The word is erring, or missing the mark, Judg. 20:16. We should all aim at an holy life, and make that our scope; but when we sin, we go beside the mark.

*{what shall I do unto thee}* If I have sinned against you, what would you have me to do to you to appease your wrath? Have I so much hurt you, that you should be so sharp upon me? Or, *what can I do unto thee*? as Psal. 11:3. what can the righteous do? If I have sinned, can I do you any harm, or work you any sorrow? Chap. 35:6,7,8. Why do you work me so much trouble then?

*{O thou preserver of men}* You who use to keep men from hurt, and do preserve others, why do you lay so great a burden upon me? God preserves beasts; Psal. 36:6. but especially man, 1 Cor. 9:9,10. Thus the word is used for keeping out of dangers, Deut. 32:10. Psal. 31:23. Or, *O thou keeper of men*. Who keeps me in afflictions, as in a prison, that I cannot escape, Lam. 3:5,6,7. I am kept in as a prisoner to judgment. See ver. 12. Or, *O thou observer of men*. You that have showed, by continual plaguing me, that you in your anger observe all men's miscarriages to take advantages against them, to bring plagues on them. As a watchman watches on a tower to see if the enemy be coming, Nah. 2:1. 2 King. 17:9. & 18:8. Job 27:18. Prov. 24:12. or as keepers watch a field, and besiegers a city, to restrain going in or out, Jer. 4:16. Thus God observed Job's sins, chap. 13:17. & 14:16. & 31:4.

*{as a mark against thee}* Heb. *to thee*. But this particle is translated against, Chap. 8:4. Psal. 51:4. You have set me right opposite against you, as a man sets a mark against him to shoot all his arrows at. Must my bosom receive all your arrows? Chap. 6:4. & 16:12. Psal. 21:12. & 38:2. 1 Sam. 3:12. You touch not others, but pick me out; as if I were the greatest sinner in the world. Suppose I have sinned, yet not so, as to deserve so great evils. Or, *as one that thou meetest withall*. As an enemy, whom you meeting in your anger are ready to devour, as a lion his prey, Chap. 13:24. & 19:11. Therefore the verb from which this word is derived in the original, is translated falling on one to destroy him, Judg. 8:21. Amos 5:19.

*{so that}* Heb. *and*. But it is well translated, so that, because God's arrows shot unto him made him burdensome to himself, Psal. 38:3,4. See the like, ver. 15.

*{I am a burden}* I am weary of my life, as of an heavy burden.

*{to myself}* Heb. *upon my self*, that lies heavy upon my shoulders, makes me troublesome to myself, and quite tires my spirits. Others read it, *to thee*. You would gladly be rid of me, as a man would of an heavy burden he is weary of, and can no longer bear, Amos 2:13. Isa. 1:24.

**V. 21. ***{And why dost thou not pardon my transgression, and take away mine iniquity? for now shall I sleep in the dust: and thou shalt seek me in the morning, and I shall not be}* Seeing my sins are no greater than other men's, why am I made a spectacle to others? why cannot my sins be passed by without such a load of troubles, that are likely to make an end of me?

*{And}* Or, *Yea*. As it is translated, Dan. 11:26. It rather becomes you a gracious God to pardon my sin, if it be so bad as they say, and to show that you have done it, by removing these plagues, than to continue them thus long, and to increase them daily.

*{Why dost thou not}* See the like expostulation, Chap. 3:11,12. and the phrase, Chap. 31:1. As if he should say; I am unequally dealt withal, who cannot be spared as well as others, that have sinned as much as I, and yet are as it were, pardoned, for they bear not the punishment of their sins so heavily as I do.

*{pardon}* Heb. *lift up*; or, take away. For pardon takes away the burden of our sins; and the knowledge thereof gives peace to our consciences, by lifting the burden off from them, that did oppress them before. Sometime the word signifies lifting up a burden or yoke to ease the oppressed; as vers. 13. Hos. 11:4. And it may be an allusion to the sacrifices and gifts that were lifted up, Ezr. 1:4. see the margin there. The sacrifices were lifted up upon the altar, Lev. 1:7,8. And thence the burnt sacrifice had his name עולה in the original, from ascending, Lev. 1:3. Sometimes it signifies taking away, Exod. 10:19. And therefore it is used for pardoning sin, which is a lifting up, and taking away the greatest burden in the world. So it is used, Psal. 85:2. Exod. 34:7. Psal. 32:1. Hos. 14:2. And the Greek word answerable to it is so used, Joh. 1:29. For Pardon see chap. 21:3. on Suffer.

*{my transgression}* Or, *rebellion*; as 2 King. 3:7. It is used in Scripture to set out great offences, as Isa. 57:4. It is a degree beyond sin, Job 34:37. If I had committed some great sin against you, as my friends say I have, yet it were fit, you should forgive me, that cannot see it, and serve you sincerely.

*{and take away}* Heb. *make to pass away*. Take away guilt and punishment, as 2 Sam. 12:13. Leave it untouched and unpunished: pass by it, as a thing you take no notice of, Amos 5:23. & 7:8. So the Greek word is used, Matth. 26:33.

*{mine iniquity}* The word properly signifies a thing bowed down, and crooked, Isa. 21:3. And metaphorically crooked and sinful actions, Dan. 9:5. And it is also used for the punishment of sin; as Gen. 4:13. Psal. 31:10. & 49:5. Gen. 19:15. And so it is used here; for Job in this distemper cast his eye more upon his punishment, than his sin. His meaning is, Why do you not take away this heavy punishment laid upon me for some notorious sin, as my friends conceive, and the world will judge?

*{for now}* If help come not quickly, it will come too late. These afflictions will soon make an end of me.

*{shall I sleep}* Or, *lye down*; for men lie down to sleep. I shall be dead. So the word is used, Chap. 3:13. Psal. 13:3. Joh. 11:11. Dan. 12:2. For Sleep see on Chap. 14:12. on Lieth down.

*{in the dust}* So the Hebrew particle ב is translated, Neh. 13:27. I shall lie in the grave the place of dust. Or, I shall sleep being brought to dust; as Gen. 3:19. Psal. 22:15. Isa. 26:19. See one verb included in another, in the Notes on Chap. 2:4. For dust see on Chap. 14:8. on Ground.

*{thou shalt seek me}* If you should seek me then, either to do me good, as you do employ the creatures next day, when the darkness of the night is over, or to afflict me more, it would be too late; therefore spare me now.

*{in the morning}* Though you should seek me diligently, as men follow their affairs in the morning. So this word is used, and translated diligently, Prov. 7:15. Or, Though you should take the fittest opportunity, and likeliest to find me out, as the morning is fittest for most serious affairs. So the morning is used, Chap. 8:5. Psal. 5:3. & 63:1. Or, Though you should soon miss me, and quickly look for me; as men do for their friends next morning, who were laid on sleep overnight, yet all were to no purpose, I could not be found alive, ver. 18.

*{and I shall not be}* See ver. 8. and on Chap. 3:16. & Psal. 39:13.

## CHAPTER 8

**Vers. 1. ***{Then answered Bildad the Shuhite, and said}* Job had as little rest now in his disputes, as Chap. 1. in the relations of his troubles. No sooner had he answered one friend, or rather enemy, but another sets upon him to vex him. It seems that Bildad was weary of hearing Job speak so much against God's proceedings, and therefore breaks him off before he had quite done speaking; as appears by his question in the next verse, How long, &c.

*{Then}* See on Chap. 4:1.

*{answered}* Bildad that he may defend God from all injustice, endeavors to defend Eliphaz his speech, and to confute Job's, and to show, that both he and his children were punished for their wickedness: and that sudden destruction will come upon all hypocrites, unless they repent, and then they may look for happiness, and all good things, as he confirms it, and proves by divers reasons. See more on Chap. 3:2. & 4:1. For Answered see on chap. 16:1.

*{Bildad the Shuhite}* See on chap. 2:11.

*{and said}* See on chap. 3:2. & 4:1. For Said see on chap. 16:1.

**V. 2. ***{How long wilt thou speak these things? and how long shall the words of thy mouth be like a strong wind?}* He shows his great dislike of Job's speeches in the two former chapters, and seems to be very zealous for God, whom he conceives Job had much dishonored in his words.

*{How long}* An angry beginning expressed by a question, (as Chap. 7:19.) accusing Job of impudency, in continuing so long in such strange speeches. He admires Job could be so large in such discourses, and shows his indignation at it, as if he could no longer bear it. See the like; Psal. 4:2. Prov. 1:22. where, as here, the question intimates a pertinacious and obstinate persistance in a way or opinion, so that one will not be altered by any sound reason, that might convince him.

*{wilt thou speak these things?}* These words may have relation to all that Job had spoken in the sixth and seventh chapters. Or they may look more nearly to what he said in the conclusion of the former chapter, vers. 19,20,21. as being the sharpest speeches against God's proceedings with him. Whichsoever it be, it argues a great admiration in Bildad, that such high words against God should come out of Job's mouth. Or, it may show forth, what a low account he makes of Job's words; as if he had said; How long will you trouble us with such sorry stuff, as this?

*{the words of thy mouth}* You are not content to nourish evil thoughts of God in your heart, but you are so bold, as to bring them forth to corrupt others, and to make them think ill of God. For Mouth see on chap. 15:5.

*{like}* This note of similitude is added, as often elsewhere in Scripture. See on Chap. 7:6. and it is often understood, where it is neither in the original, nor in the translation, as Chap. 6:12. & 7:7.

*{a strong wind}* He reproves the vehemency of Job's oration, which coming out of a strong passion of mind was so pathetical, and of so high a strain, as if he would have blown down God, and all his proceedings at a breath: such a blustering noise did his words make, as if they were like a vehement wind, that bears down all before it; which he might the more wonder at in Job, that such great words should come out of so weak and worn a carcass. So false teachers with swelling words blow down whole houses, Tit. 1:11. and ignorant persons are carried away with every wind of doctrine, Eph. 4:14. Such stout words the wicked Jews spake against God, Mal. 3:13. Or it may be understood of the length of Job's speech, which was like a long lasting blustering wind, and so it answers fitly to the beginning of the verse. *How long wilt thou speak?* Your speech is so fluent in this argument; and you are so full of words, that you will not give any man leave to confute you. For it seems Bildad was fain to interrupt him, or else he could not have been heard. See on ver. 1. The word will bear either of these senses, for it imports both magnitude and multitude, big words, or many words. See Chap. 35:16. and 15:10. For Wind see on chap. 15:2. on Vain.

**V. 3. ***{Doth God pervert judgment? or doth the Almighty pervert justice?}* In this verse is laid down the ground of Bildad's following discourse, which afterwards he applies to Job in particular. He labors to prove, that God deals uprightly in giving good things to good men, and punishing evil men; and concludes from this general course of God's dealings, that Job was justly afflicted. Your words run, as if you, O Job, did accuse God of doing you wrong, in laying these plagues upon you. Your speeches look that way, as if you did indeed think so, though you dare not say so in plain terms. But though God be אל strong and שדי almighty, yet will he not do any man wrong.

*{Doth}* The question intimates a great admiration in Bildad, that Job should have such a strange conceit of God, and a vehement negation of the thing. Certainly God will not wrong any man. You must take this for a grounded truth, although your afflictions may blind you, and make you to think otherwise. If it were another man's case you would confess it. Or, can you give any instance, that ever God did any man wrong? If not, (as I know you cannot) why do you then think so hardly of him? Or, *Should God, &c*. Is it a thing becoming God to wrong any man? Is it not sinful to have such a suspicion of so holy a God? Should he not then cease to be God, if he should not keep the rule of justice and equity? See Gen. 18:25. Deut. 32:4. 2 Chr. 19:7. Rom. 3:5,6. Dan. 9:14.

*{God}* This name is taken from strength in the original, and is fitly applied to God, in whom and from whom is all strength. And especially in matters of justice, which men for want of strength, cannot administer at sometimes; as David could not on Joab and Abishai, 2 Sam. 3:39. But God cannot be hindered from doing justice on the greatest, because he is strong, yea Almighty; as follows in this verse.

*{pervert}* Heb. *make crooked*. So the word signifies, Eccles. 1:15. It is impossible that God should not proceed according to the straight rule of justice. Or, *overthrow*. So the word is translated, Chap. 19:6. Lam. 3:36. When judgment is overthrown, a man is wronged in his cause, which God will not do, to whom men appeal, when they are so wronged by others, Lam. 3:59.

*{judgment}* The word is sometime put for doing men right, as Isa. 5:7. and sometime for the law, the rule of judging, Psal. 19:9. and otherwhile for moderation, as Jer. 10:24. But when it is joined with justice, (as here) it imports care in finding out the equity of the cause. God does as surely take notice of the business before he pass sentence, as judges do by hearing, or witnesses by seeing, Exod. 3:7. Gen. 18:21. God is so careful to proceed warily in judging the cause, that he will never wrong any any man. For Judgment see on chap. 22:4.

*{the Almighty}* See on Chap. 5:17.

*{pervert}* See before in this verse.

*{justice}* Judgment and justice go often together in Scripture. See 2 Sam. 8:15. 1 King. 10:9. Psal. 119:121. As judgment is distinguished from justice; judgment is used in finding out the right; justice, in passing sentence according to it. God will search out the equity of the cause, and proceed accordingly. He has no need to receive ought of men to corrupt him, who is all-sufficient. It were sin to think he would do so.

**V. 4. ***{If thy children have sinned against him, and he have cast them away for their transgression}* God does not pervert judgment, for your children were overthrown for their sin: else they had escaped. And he would prosper you, if you were righteous, vers. 5,6.

*{If}* Or, *Although*; as Chap. 27:16. Though your children perished for their transgression, yet God has been more merciful to you, in giving you time to humble yourself: which if you do, you shall escape and prosper. It is not a supposition, but a true story, Chap. 1:19. And therefore it should the rather be read, *Although*. For it was done, and for their sin done, as Bildad thought.

*{thy children}* Heb. *thy sons*: Yet the daughters are comprehended, who perished with them, Chap. 1:18,19. For Children see on chap. 14:21. on Sons.

*{have sinned}* See on chap. 7:20.
