*{against him}* Heb. *to him*. See on chap. 7:20. or, unto him. As this particle is used, Nehem. 2:7. Though men saw not how they carried themselves in their feasts, nor were not hurt by it, yet God saw it, and was offended for some great evil in their meetings, else Bildad conceives he would not have destroyed them with so heavy a judgment.

*{and he have cast them away for their transgression}* So this particle ב is translated, for, Est. 4:7. Psal. 150:2. Isa. 57:17. Gen. 18:28. & 29:25. & 31:42. In Hebrew it is, *for the hand of their transgression*. That is, for their iniquities; as ביד is used, Isa. 64:7. and it may further intimate their actual wickedness. That they were not only evil in heart, but also in life. Hands do often, in Scripture phrase, intend men's actions: as Psal. 58:2. Isa. 1:12,15. Job 11:14. Bildad's meaning is, that God had destroyed Job's children, as a man breaks an earthen vessel, and dashes it to pieces, by casting it violently to the ground; and that God did this for their extraordinary sinful courses known to God, though hidden from the world. He thought that God did all this out of wrath against Job's children only, never imagining that Satan had any hand in it, by God's permission to try Job. Sometimes the particle ב signifies the instrument, and is translated By. And By the hand, is by the means; as Psal. 77:20. And then the sense were, that Job's sons should be destroyed by some wicked plot of their own, as Haman was. But that cannot be, because they were destroyed by a storm of wind: and that Bildad knew well: and therefore that is not his meaning. It may be read, *And he have sent them forth into the hand of their transgression*. And then sending forth, is putting them out of his protection, as Adam was sent forth out of paradise, Gen. 3:23. and delivering them over to be punished for their sins. So one verb or action is included in another, Psal. 89:39. *Thou hast profane his crown to the ground*, that is, by casting it to the ground, as it is in the translation. So Isa. 38:14. Looking, added; and verse 17. delivered, added, as included in the former words, and so the sense is made plain. So it would be here, if it were read, *And he have sent them forth, delivering them into the hands of their transgressions*. See more on Chap. 7:21. Into the hand; for, into the power. See Neh. 9:27. Joel 3:8. And hand for power, Gen. 16:6. & 39:6. Job 1:12. & 2:6. Of their transgression: or, Of their rebellion. It properly signifies a servant's receding from the will of his master, out of pride, 2 King. 3:7. & 8:20. Some understand this latter part of the verse, of God's giving them over to their own hearts' lusts, that they might go on in sin with greediness, as Psal. 81:12. But that cannot be Bildad's meaning here, for he points at the violent death of Job's sons, which cut them off from going on in their sinful courses. Rather sin is here compared to an executioner, and God to a judge, who gives men over to suffer condign punishment for their sin: and that is called, Sending them forth into the hand of their sins, as if their sins did execute that sentence on them, which they deserved by their sins, Psal. 140:11. Prov. 5:22. For Cast away see on Chap. 14:20. on Sendest. For Hand see on Chap. 15:23.

**V. 5. ***{If thou wouldest seek unto God betimes, and make thy supplication to the Almighty}* God has indeed punished your children for their sins, and delivered them over to the last and lasting judgment, without hope of mercy, because of those enormous wickednesses they have have committed against him. It seems you have not committed so great transgressions against God, and therefore he has spared you hitherto, that the sight of our wholesome counsel, might bring you to seek unto God for mercy; and then he that destroyed your sons, will not only spare you, but restore you to your former prosperity.

*{If thou wouldest seek betimes}* The Hebrew word signifies seeking early in the morning, making it his chiefest, and most serious work; as men that have business of weight, neglect no time, but go about it in the morning, as soon as they are up. For the phrase, see on Chap. 7:18,21. For the matter, see Chap. 5:8. & 11:13. Acts 17:27.

*{unto God}* See on vers. 3.

*{and make thy supplication}* The Hebrew word signifies praying for mercy out of free grace alone. He counsels Job not to stand upon terms of his own innocency with God, much less to plead any merit; but to throw himself at God's feet, and to sue to him for mercy, for the pardon of his sins, and taking away his heavy judgments, deservedly inflicted upon him. So the word is used, Deut. 2:23. It is the language of the poor, that have no merit to plead, but sue for relief out of mere favor, Prov. 18:23.

*{to the Almighty}* See on vers. 3. & Chap. 5:17.

**V. 6. ***{If thou wert pure and upright, surely now he would awake for thee, and make the habitation of thy righteousness prosperous}* If having obtained pardon for your sins past, by humble and earnest prayer to God, you would amend for the time to come, God would deal better with you.

*{If thou wert}* Or, *If thou wouldest be*. If you would join reformation to your supplication, and did resolve, and would endeavor to alter your course for time to come, God would surely bless you. So the phrase would suit well with what went before in the fifth verse; *if thou wouldest seek unto God*, and with that which follows after, *He would awake for thee*.

*{pure}* Clean. Shining like gold purified from the dross, Lam. 4:7. Levit. 24:2. Matth. 5:16. Free from filth and spots of great sins, either open or secret, and unrepented of; Chap. 9:30. & 15:15. Psal. 73:13. Therefore sin is called filthiness, 2 Cor. 7:1. Eph. 5:4. Jam. 1:21. 1 Pet. 3:21. Rev. 17:4. It is also compared to spots, Eph. 5:27. 2 Pet. 2:13. Jud. vers. 12.

*{upright}* Or, right. Not crooked. Did walk according to the straight rule of God's word, and not according to your own crooked affections. See on Chap. 1:1. Purity here may signify sincerity of heart, and uprightness, integrity of life.

*{surely}* So this particle כי signifies, Numb. 22:33. Ruth 1:10. without any doubt it is so. God would certainly show you mercy, if you did repent and reform. There is no question to be made of it. The words might be more fully expressed, thus; It is certain that he would awake for you.

*{now}* Presently, speedily, as soon as you repent, Jer. 31:18,19,20. Luke 15:20.

*{he would awake}* Though now he seem to be asleep unto you, and not to regard your prayer, yet if you did add reformation to it, he would quickly hear you, and grant you the good you pray for. God never sleeps Psal. 121:4. But when he lets his people lie in troubles, and seems not to regard them, he is compared to a man asleep: and then his people do, as it were, awake him with their prayers, as Christ was awaked in the ship, Matth. 8:25. Psal. 7:6. & 44:23.

*{for thee}* To help you, deliver you, and do you good. Not awake against you to do you hurt as he has formerly done, and then you might justly fear, he might do still for your sins, Dan. 9:24.

*{and make}* He would not awake for nothing, but for your good, not only to free you from your trouble, but also to make you prosper, Chap. 5:24.

*{the habitation}* Some take it for the soul, in which righteousness dwells. Others, for the body, wherein the righteous soul dwells. But it is to be understood plainly of the place where he dwelt, with the persons and goods belonging to him, and business there to be done; all which God would prosper. For Habitation see on Chap. 5:3.

*{of thy righteousness}* That is, your righteous habitation: as O God of my righteousnesse, Psal. 4:1. that is, O my righteous God. Your habitation, which you shall have righteously gotten, and not by rapine and extortion, as you did your former: (for so Bildad thought:) or, the habitation wherein you shall exercise duties of righteousness to God and man for time to come, for it is not here opposed to holiness, but contains holiness under it. So the word is used, Prov. 8:8,18,20. & 16:31. Eccl. 7:15.

*{Prosperous}* The original word in the first signification of it, is, peaceable. Now because prosperity follows peace, as ruin follows war; therefore the word, at the second hand, signifies prosperous. He will make it a comfortable habitation, and all that you have in it, or about it, to prosper, Psal. 1:3. Your family shall live peaceably among themselves; and you shall have abundance, and not be robbed by others, as formerly. Your habitation has been filled with goods gotten by oppression, and subject to spoil. If you repent, it shall be so no more; but be filled with store of goods righteously gotten. Lastly, because in peace, buildings and trades are brought to perfection, that are ruined by war; the word signifies perfect, or complete. And then the sense may be, Your habitation is now ruinous, your children, cattle and goods lost, but upon your returning to God, he will not only begin to build it again, but make it as complete as ever it was. He will finish it, so that nothing shall be wanting to it.

**V. 7. ***{Though thy beginning was small, yet thy latter end should greatly increase}* God will not only awake for you, and make your state prosperous, as he did before; but he will also increase it very much; as afterwards he did. See Chap. 42.

*{though}* Heb. *and*. But it is often put for though; as Psal. 78:23. Prov. 28:6.

*{thy beginning}* Not the beginning of his life, but of his riches: the first part or head of your estate. The Hebrew word ראשית signifies 1. A beginning in time. Gen. 10:10. 2. First in dignity. 1 Sam. 15:21. Here it is taken in the first sense, for first in time.

*{was}* In former times; or, were; or, should be. Now after your repentance.

*{small}* In quantity or quality, riches or honor; for greatness and smallness are applied to both in Scripture, Psal. 119:141. Jer. 30:19. & 49:15. & 42:18.

*{yet}* So ו is translated, Psal. 119:109,110. The smallness of your beginning shall be no hindrance to your increase in riches and greatness. But you shall grow apace, when once you begin to thrive again.

*{thy latter end}* Not of your days, but of your wealth. You shall grow to a great height of riches and honor before you die. The word אחרית signifies, 1. An end in time. Deut. 4:30. 2. The reward which is given, when the work is ended, Prov. 24:14. 3. Posterity which come after, and succeed their parents, Psal. 109:13. 4. The lowest, or last in honor, most contemptible. Jer. 50:12. Here it is put in the first sense.

*{should greatly increase}* Some take it for a comparison between his past and future prosperity; as if Bildad should have said; Your former riches seemed to be great, but they were nothing in comparison of those that are to come, if you truly return to God. But it is more likely to be a description of the greatness of his future prosperity, notwithstanding the smallness of his hopes: as if Bildad had said; You have nothing left now; all is gone; yet if you be reconciled to God, he will enrich you to admiration. So it came to pass, Chap. 42. Each of his friends gave him a small pittance to begin withal, and God increased it to the double of his former possessions.

**V. 8. ***{For enquire I pray thee of the former age, and prepare thyself to the search of their fathers}* I do not desire that you should ground your belief on my words; but enquire of the wisest and best men in former ages, whom you have no reason to distrust, although you may slight me. They will by many and daily examples prove, both what I said before, that God will make good men to prosper after their afflictions: and what I shall say after, that he will destroy the wicked, though they flourish for a time.

*{For}* This particle ushers in a reason of that which went before from the experience of former times.

*{enquire}* Heb. *ask*; Seek to be resolved out of the writings of predecessors, or the instructions and informations they have left to their posterity by word of mouth. The Hebrew word שאל signifies 1. To ask, or enquire. Gen. 38:21. 2. To borrow. Exod. 12:35. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{I pray thee}* He deals gently with him, as Eliphaz did Chap. 4:7. whom Bildad imitates in matter, and manner, in arguments and expressions.

*{of}* Ask them how things went then. So ל is used, Gen. 43:27. or concerning: Enquire how matters were carried then. So it is taken, Psal. 64:5. & 91:11.

*{the former}* Some take it for the first age of the world: as the word is translated, Exod. 12:2. But that cannot be the sense here, because their fathers are mentioned, which agrees not with the first age of the world. It must therefore be understood of the age immediately before them, whose sayings and doings were yet fresh in the memory of their children yet alive. So the word signifies, Deut. 4:32. & 10:14.

*{age}* An age signifies a multitude of men, that lived long either at one time: and is translated sometimes a generation; as Psal. 12:7. & 109:13. & 145:13. Numb. 32:13. Learn from the men that lived then, whether I say true or no. Sometimes it signifies the time in which men live together; as Gen. 6:9. & 9:12. Search the chronicles of those times, and they will confirm what I say.

*{prepare thyself}* Or, *Fit thy self*. Go not rashly about it, but lay a good foundation of your search, and fix your heart on it; Psal. 108:1.

*{to the search}* To search out what they believed in this point Chap. 11:7. or to conceive that which they have found out by diligent search.

*{of their fathers}* The fathers of the immediate forepassing times; and so upwards to the beginning of the world. But these only named, because men's informations and memories will reach better to two generations, in regard of the long life of some particular persons in each, than further backward: Go backward from one generation to another, and you shall find it to have been so always, by that which was done in their fathers' time, and in every age observed by them. Now the fathers who lived before, had more means to get knowledge of the truth, in regard of the length of their lives, whereby they were filled with experience; and also because God himself sometimes spake unto them, and those truths they received from their parents were kept purer, because they were nearer to the fountains, and therefore they appealed often to them before the Scriptures were written, as our laws refer to ruled cases long since determined, Deut. 4:32. & 32:7. Job 4:7. & 5:1. & 32:7. But now the word is the rule, and not revelations nor traditions, Isa. 8:20. Rev. 22:18,19. For Father see on chap. 15:24.

**V. 9. ***{For we are but of yesterday, and know nothing, because our days upon earth are a shadow}* In this verse Bildad answers a secret objection, which Job might have made to this purpose; What need you send us back to our predecessors to school? Why cannot you teach us yourself? He answers, we know not what was done formerly, we have had but a short time of observation; and therefore we refer you to those that lived long since, and longer, and so might know more than we.

*{we}* We who live now in this age: or I and my fellows, who reason with you. See on Chap. 4:2.

*{of yesterday}* Sometimes the word תמול in Scripture, is put for the day immediately before; as Exod. 5:14. Josh. 4:3. Sometimes it signifies all time past; as Heb. 13:8. In this place it signifies time newly past. We are but of late time. And therefore it is well translated, *But of yesterday*, to point out the sense more fully. We were born lately, and have lived but a while in the world, and therefore cannot know much, nor are not to be compared with our fathers, who were born before us, and lived longer than we, Gen. 47:9. Thus yesterday is put for time lately past, 2 King. 9:26. 2 Sam. 15:20. and so the same word is translated of late, Mic. 2:8. or, we are as ignorant, in comparison of those wise men that lived in former times, as children, that were born yesterday, that know nothing.

*{and}* Or, *therefore*. Being so lately born, we have not time to get knowledge by study or experience. So this particle ו is translated, Ezek. 23:31. Job 11:6.

*{know nothing}* Heb. know not. Not that they were beasts, or silly idiots, that had no understanding of the truth; but little, in comparison of what their predecessors knew. So much, in Scripture phrase is sometimes signified by a negation; as Hos. 6:6. I desire mercy and not sacrifice; that is, I delight more in mercy, than sacrifice.

*{because}* Another reason of the shortness of their knowledge taken from the shortness of their days. As they lived not in former days to know, what was done then; so they had lived but a while, nor could not live long to get any great store of knowledge now.

*{our days}* The days of our life: See on Chap. 7:vers. 1,6. For Dayes see on Chap. 14:14.

*{upon earth}* While we live here in this world. See on Chap. 7:1. For Earth see on Chap. 9:24.

*{are a shadow}* They are like to a shadow. The note of likeness is often omitted in Scripture to make the speech the more emphatical; as ver. 6. See on Chap. 7:7,9. It may be understood of their days past, which were soon gone; or of the whole course of their life, which was very short; not like a day that consists of many hours, but like a shadow that lasts but a small part of a day, and is gone as soon as the sun hides his head under a cloud, 1 Chr. 29:15. Job 7:6. Psal. 144:4. & 102:11. & 39:5,6.

**V. 10. ***{Shall not they teach thee, and tell thee, and utter words out of their heart?}* You shall not enquire in vain, nor lose your labor, you shall learn much from them. They will teach you the same things you hear of me.

*{shall not}* Certainly they shall inform you in what you could not inform yourself. This is the force of the question, Gen. 13:9. See on vers. 3.

*{they}* The forefathers spoken of, vers. 8. to which coherence he now returns, having digressed in the ninth verse, in a parenthesis.

*{teach thee}* Distill their precepts into you gently; as the rain falls upon the earth to make it fruitful; for so the word signifies in its primary use; Prov. 11:25. Hos. 10:12. Deut. 32:2. They shall acquaint you with God's wisdom, and justice, in his proceedings, by those instructions they have left with their posterity; for they themselves were dead. For Teach see on Chap. 6:24.

*{and tell thee}* Or, *and say to thee*; to wit, the words in the verses following. They shall not hold their peace, and engross their knowledge to themselves. For Tell see on Chap. 16:1. on Said.

*{and utter words}* Heb. *and make words to come forth*. To flow as from a fountain, or come out of the heart, as rain out of the clouds.

*{out of their heart}* A wise man's heart is his treasury, wherein he lays up abundance of knowledge to bring forth upon all occasions, Mat. 12:35. & 13:52. and old men have most time to lay up this treasure: they will not speak rashly, or at random, as you have done, but what they have well weighed in their hearts, before it comes out of their mouths. They will speak judiciously and solidly to you: they will teach you really and sincerely, what they think themselves. For Heart see on Chap. 15:12.

**V. 11. ***{Can the rush grow up without mire? can the flag grow without water?}* Now he sets down what the fathers had observed, and would impart unto them; and that by similitudes taken from natural things, showing thereby that the same God is the author both of nature and grace, when the mysteries of the latter are expounded by the works of the former. And as David and Solomon were excellent in this way of teaching; so likewise were Job and his friends. By three elegant similitudes, one taken from a rush, another from a spider's web, and a third from a tree, he sets out the flitting prosperity of ungodly men: and strikes at Job and his children for want of solid piety, and closely intimates, that therefore God's judgments came upon them.

*{can}* Certainly it cannot. See on vers. 3. and 10.

*{the rush}* It is as ordinary for God to destroy great men, that have no piety in them; as for a rush to wither, when the mire in which it grows, is dried up. The word is translated, a bulrush, Exod. 2:3. Isa. 18:2. It has the name from drinking, because rushes and flags cannot subsist without moisture. It seems to be some bigger and stronger matter, than our rushes; because vessels to keep children out of the water, and for passengers to go by sea, were made of it; as appears in the places quoted.

*{grow up}* The growing here is meant in height. Lift up its head stately. For the word is often used for pride in Scripture, Prov. 15:25. Isa. 16:6. A rush cannot pride itself without moisture; nor a wicked man lift up himself long above others, for want of God's favor to uphold him.

*{without mire}* The word is translated fens, Chap. 40:21. Miry places, Ezek. 47:11. wet and soft grounds produce and nourish rushes.

*{the flag}* Some take it for a lesser reed. Others, for a meadow; as Gen. 41:2. meaning the grass that is on the meadow, on which cattle feed. The word has its original from אח, a brother; because flags, and so also grass in a meadow, grow together as in a brotherhood.

*{grow}* Multiply, as flags and grass do. So much the word imports, vers. 7. Psal. 73:12. or grow in height and bigness; as Isa. 17:11.

*{without water}* It cannot grow, nor thrive in dry places. No more can ungodly men prosper long, though they may flourish for a time. For Water see on Chap. 14:9.

**V. 12. ***{Whilst it is yet in his greenness, and not cut down, it withereth before any other herb}* He goes on with the similitude, to show that as rushes when they are greenest, and spared by men, and permitted to grow; yet wither often for want of moisture: so the prosperity of wicked men, when it seems most flourishing, if it be not ruined by others, yet for want of God's blessing, it moulders away of itself? which has been often seen in our days, in great estates unjustly gotten; and consumed, no man knows how, Hag. 1:6. This verse may be joined to the former, and be read thus; Is the Rush grow up without mire, or the flag without water, while it is yet in his greenness, though it be not cut down; yet it will wither before any other herb. So ו is put for if, Chap. 4:2. And then the sense is, Suppose a reed or flag should grow green, where no moisture is, yet it is so far from continuing, that if no violence be offered to it, yet it will decay of itself; and so will wicked men's wealth, though God suffer them to thrive for a time.

*{whilst it is yet in his greenness}* That is, while it flourishes most, before the beauty of it be gone. Hence the month Abib, or March, had his name in the Hebrew, because in those Eastern countries, corn did then begin to appear green.

*{and not cut down}* It is translated, cutting up, Chap. 30:4. cropping off, Ezek. 17:4,22. plucking, Deut. 23:25. The Jewish writers conceive it to be a gathering with the hand; not a cutting down with any iron instrument. However, it imports, that it was not cropped by any violence, but decayed of itself.

*{it withereth}* It is dried up for want of water, which is the drink thereof, Psal. 129:6. Jer. 17:6.

*{before}* The Hebrew word usually signifies, Before in place, that is, in sight. And so it may be here; it decays while other herbs, growing about it, do as it were stand looking upon it, admiring at the sudden decay of it. So actions of life are attributed to lifeless creatures. See on Chap. 1:19. yet it is commonly understood, here, of withering before other herbs in time: Lev. 18:27. the word is so used.

*{any}* See on Chap. 1:11.

*{other}* This word is not in the original; but is added to make the sense the plainer.

*{herb}* It properly signifies such things as spring out of the earth of themselves for food for the beasts, 1 King. 18:5. though it may hold up the head higher, and flourish more, yet it sooner decays than any other green thing growing out of the earth in a drought.

**V. 13. ***{So are the paths of all that forget God; and the hypocrite's hope shall perish}* He now applies the former similitude: As it is with the forementioned rush, just so it is with wicked men: though for a time they prosper, and seem to be upheld by God's favor, as the rush by moisture; yet not having it, they fall of themselves, before any violent death seize on them.

*{so are the paths}* A metaphor from travelers, to set out the beginning, progress, and end of ungodly men. He means it not of their actions, as the word is used, Psal. 1:6. & 119:1. but of their life, riches, power, state, flourishing condition, sudden death, and dissolution, ver. 19. Though they grow from a low estate to an high one, and flourish for a time; yet their estate suddenly changes, and they are destroyed before others.

*{of all}* No difference: God is alike in his proceedings, and is no accepter of persons, Deut. 10:16,17,18.

*{that forget God}* That forget him in their thoughts, and think seldom of him, Psal. 10:4. or show, that they remember him not, by disobeying him in their actions, and in the course of their lives, Psal. 10:4,5. & 14:1. & 36:1,2. Deut. 8:11. Psal. 50:22. Or, that esteem lightly of God; for such things as we esteem lightly of, we quickly forget, Jer. 2:32. & 30:14. Or, such as depart from God; for we soon forget our friends that are gone. Out of sight, and out of mind. Eph. 2:12. Heb. 12:5. Forgetfulness of God is a mark of a wicked man in Scripture, Psal. 9:17. Such, if they thrive, trust not in God, but in their own wit, power, and riches, Deut. 8:14,19. For the name of God, see on vers. 3.

*{and}* Or, *for*. Here is a reason of the likeness of the wicked man's hope to the rush or flag, because it suddenly decays, as they do. So this particle is translated, Chap. 21:14. & 28:18.

*{the hypocrites}* Such as are covered with fair shows, though they be foul within; as Chap. 13:16. Isa. 21:7. & 10:6. And then he secretly accuses Job and his children of hypocrisy, that they were like painted sepulchers, fair without, but very foul within, Mat. 23:27. For they knew that Job was not a gross wicked man. So Zophar presses Job with hypocrisy, Chap. 20:5. as if he had been one that was wicked before God in heart, but counterfeited piety before men to deceive them.

*{hope}* He shall give over hoping, when he sees, he cannot get what he hoped for; to wit, stability and firmness in his prosperous condition, which seemed to himself, and others, likely to abide. Or, he shall not have the thing he hoped for. See on Chap. 4:6. התקוה signifies. 1. Line. Josh. 2:18. 2. Hope. Zech. 9:12. So here.

*{shall perish}* When a man cannot attain what he hopes for, his hope is said to perish, though he may expect still, but to no purpose, vers. 14. & 14:19. So shall wicked men's hope vanish, Chap. 11:20. & 18:14. Prov. 10:28.

**V. 14. ***{Whose hope shall be cut off, and whose trust shall be a spider's web}* He goes on to give a reason, why the hope of the wicked shall perish, because God will frustrate it, and sets it out by another similitude, taken from that which all men know to be brittle, a spider's web.

*{whose}* He spake of them in the plural number before; now in the singular: intimating, that the hope of every hypocrite flourishing shall be cut off. None shall escape, or fare better than other. Others read it, *For his hope, &c*. So the word is used, Jer. 32:3. And then he proves the former assertion by a new argument, and does not barely proceed in the relation.

*{hope}* So the word כסל signifies, Job 31:24. Prov. 3:26. and it answers well to the former verse: where see the sense of it, though the word differ in the original. Others read it *Folly*; as the same word is translated, Psal. 49:13. Eccl. 7:25. Not that they should cease to be foolish (for that were a blessing, not a curse) but that prosperity, which they in their folly thought would last ever, shall be cut off. The word is also translated Flanke. Chap. 15:27. But not so here.

*{shall be cut off}* So יקוט is rendered, Job 10:1. in the margin. And then it points at God's judgment upon Job, whose children and goods did not moulder away by little and little, but were suddenly taken from him by God's judgments. But the word is most commonly translated loathing, as Ezek. 6:9. & 20:43. And so this place may be read, *Whose hope shall loath him*; or, be loathsome to him. He shall be weary of looking for the thing he hopes for; or it shall go away from him, as from an abominable thing.

*{and whose trust}* Hope consists in expectation of some good. Trust in depending on some other for it. The hypocrite hoped that his prosperity would continue, and trusted in his riches and power for the continuance of it. But both these would fail him. He shall trust in vain. The thing he trusts in will deceive him, and then his expectation must needs perish.

*{shall be a spider's web}* Heb. *a spiders house*. For the spider builds it to dwell in, as men do houses. He does not say, it is as a spider's web, but leaves out the note of similitude, to set out the vanity and brittleness of it the more. See on Chap. 7:9. Though his state seems to be very strong and stable, yet it is very frail and weak, and easily broken. As spiders cannot defend themselves from wrong in their web, no more can wicked men prevent destruction by their wealth. See Isa. 59:5,6. Spiders' webs are swept down, so shall they be destroyed by God's judgments. For House, see on Chap. 15:28.

**V. 15. ***{He shall lean upon his house, but it shall not stand: he shall hold it fast, but it shall not endure}* In this verse is laid down the application to the former similitude, taken from the spider's web. As it fails the spider's confidence, so shall the hypocrite's habitation fail him.

*{He}* Some understand it of the spider; but it is to be understood of the hypocrite himself.

*{shall lean}* He shall think to lie hid safely in his habitation, or possessions, and therein to be free from perils; but it will fail him, as a man that leans on a weak staff or reed, falls to the ground, Isa. 36:6. Ezek. 29:6,7. Bildad might think Job had put too much confidence in his possessions, which had been very great, and therefore the former losses came upon him.

*{upon his house}* The hypocrite has an house to lean on, as well as the spider. But the spider's leaning is with the body, the hypocrite's with the mind. Hereby is meant his trust, mentioned, ver. 14. And by his house, is meant his riches, and whatever he has in his house, as Esth. 8:7. for hypocrites trust not in bare walls, but in their treasure hid in their houses.

*{but it shall not stand}* Not abide: as the word is used, Psal. 119:90. It shall fall itself, and therefore shall not be able to uphold him. He shall lose his possessions he trusts in, and himself perish with them. The spider's web will fall with the weight of the spider's leaning on it, though there be no wind to blow it away. And hypocrites ruin their own houses by their sins, and particularly by relying on them.

*{he shall hold it fast}* Or, *he shall lay fast hold on it*. He shall not only lean on it, as a weary man leans against a wall, but also lay fast hold on it, as a man ready to be drowned, does on a bough of a tree to save his life, as if he would never let it go, but meant always to be upheld by it. A man may lean, and not lay hold on the thing he leans on; but by laying hold on it, he means, accounting to make it firm to him, and him to it: as returning it to uphold him, Job 2:9. & 27:5. The hypocrite thinks he has his prosperity fast, and shall be sure to continue in it.

*{but it shall not endure}* Heb. *not rise, or not stand*; to wit, in judgment, as Psal. 1:5. So Esth. 9:29,31,32. To confirm: Heb. to make to rise, or, stand. Laws are for continuance, and men keep their possessions by law. But the hypocrite has, in regard of God, no right to his, and so shall lose it. For Rise, see on Chap. 14:12.

**V. 16. ***{He is green before the Sun, and his branch shooteth forth in his garden}* Here the hypocrite is compared to a flourishing tree, which is stronger than a rush, or spider's web, yet cannot continue long. This verse sets out the height of the hypocrite's prosperity: the next points at his security: the 18. at his ruin, under the similitude of a tree, as appears in the 19. verse.

*{he is green}* Or, *moist*: full of juices, as Chap. 24:8. So the hypocrite seems strong.

*{before the Sun}* Before the sun rises. So the word is used for precedency in time, vers. 12. and Exod. 20:14. The hypocrite thrives extraordinarily, as a tree that grows without the help of the sun: or, Before the sun; that is, by the benefit of the sunbeams, and heat of it. So hypocrites thrive so fast, as if God's blessing did wonderfully attend upon them. Or in despite of the scorching beams of the sun; as being so full of juice, that the beams of the sun cannot dry it up. So hypocrites seem to thrive, as it were, against God's will, whom they daily blaspheme: and in sight of all the plots of their adversaries to ruin them, Psal. 37:35. Or, in the sight of the sun, that is, openly, so that all men take notice of their wonderful prosperity. So this phrase is used 2 Sam. 12:12. The word in the original, put here for the sun, is taken from a word that signifies to minister or serve, because the sun is a public servant to minister light and heat to all the world, Psal. 19:6.

*{and his branch}* This shows plainly, as also the place following, the garden, that there is a comparison taken from a tree, though it be not named expressly. יונקת comes from a word that signifies to suck, because the branches, new shot forth, are tender, like to sucking children: and also they suck moisture from the root, as children do milk from their mother's breasts. We call some kind of branches suckers. And by the hypocrite's branch is meant here, his offspring, honors, and riches, Chap. 14:7. & 15:32. Ezek. 17:22. Hos. 14:6.

*{shooteth forth}* Heb. *goeth forth*. But so, that it argues an increase and multiplication of riches, as boughs sprout plentifully out of a tree. For Shooteth forth, see on Chap. 15:20. on Go out. And on Chap. 20:25. on Cometh out.

*{in}* As Isa. 24:22. Or, *over*. To show his abundant prosperity, like trees, whose branches shoot forth beyond the fence of the orchard, and bear abundance of fruit.

*{his garden}* The place where trees use to grow. So does the hypocrite thrive in his habitation, which is his enclosed place, where he has fenced himself in, as in a garden, and thinks he cannot be hurt in it, or pulled out of it. So much is imported in the derivation of the word, from גנן, to protect, Cant. 4:12. Eccl. 2:5,6. He makes a very glorious show in his outward pomp, and lives as in a paradise, Gen. 2:8. Some think he begins to speak of the godly man here, and read the 18. verse; Shall any man destroy him out of his place, so that it will deny him, &c? No it cannot be: His prosperity will hold, when the hypocrite's decays. And so the issue of both is summed up in the 20. verse; from whence the antecedent to He, in this 16. verse, to wit, the perfect man, mentioned there, is taken.

**V. 17. ***{His roots are wrapped about the heap, and he seeth the place of stones}* He goes on to set out the prosperous estate of the hypocrite for a time, whose destruction is mentioned in the following verse.

*{His roots}* That whereby he thinks himself to be fastened, as a tree is by the roots. For he not only seems glorious to others, but thinks himself firm and safe also in his habitation.

*{are wrapped}* Fold themselves to and fro; or, are wreathed in with the heap.

*{about}* Compass*in*g the heap round about on the outside. Or, in; as the word is used in ver. 16. & Dan. 11:20. Or, *with*: as Job 38:32. The branches are folded in with the heap, as thorns in a hedge, one within another, so as they can hardly be parted. This shows the seeming firmness of the hypocrite's prosperity.

*{the heap}* An heap of stones: as follows in this verse. So it signifies, Gen. 31:46,47. Some take it for a tree, growing by the wall of the house; and by folding the roots in with the foundation, weakening the house, and therefore pulled down by the master of the house, as verse 18. Others, for a tree that is not rooted in loose earth; where it soon may be blown down, but among stones and rocks incorporating itself into them; and yet grows in spite of stones, to signify the stability and growth of the hypocrite's prosperous condition for a season. Or, *The Spring, or fountain*; as Josh. 15:19. A space where springs arise, and spring out, and wind about the roots of the tree, or run between them, where no water is wanting. So also this word is translated, Cant. 4:12. For a spring has plenty of water in it, as an heap has stones. And so it sets out the thriving condition of the hypocrite. The word גל signifies. 1. An heap, Gen. 31:46. 2. A fountain, Cant. 4:12. 3. A wave of the sea, Isa. 48:18. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{and he seeth}* The hypocrite discerns it: Or, *it seeth*. That is, the tree, which spreads its roots among the stones underground, and might see them, if it had eyes, though they lie hid under the earth out of the sight of men. A metaphor: See on Chap. 1:18.

*{place}* Heb. *house*. So it is put for a place, 2 Sam. 15:27. For House, see on Chap. 15:28.

*{of stones}* They that understand it of the tree, take it for the place where stones lie buried underground, or the vault about a fountain, which the roots of trees will reach to. They who understand it of the hypocrite, take it for a stony place to build an house in, where he may lay a sure foundation, or for a house of stone to dwell in, that is, a strong house, or for a stone wall to fence in his house, goods and cattle; or for an high place to overlook all his neighbors. All tending to show forth the greatness of his pride, security, and prosperity. For Stones, see on Chap. 14:19.

**V. 18. ***{If he destroy him from his place, then it shall deny him, saying, I have not seen thee}* Bildad goes on from his descriptions of the great height of the hypocrite's prosperity, to his sudden and irrecoverable punishment, and utter ruin.

*{If he}* They which take it of the tree, understand the sun, mentioned ver. 16. which by his scorching beams, dries up the moisture about the tree, and makes it wither. Others, according to the usual phrase of the language, understand the destroyer, the nominative case being included in the verb; as Gen. 50:26. They embalmed Joseph, that is, the embalmers embalmed him. So here, If he destroy him, that is, If the destroyer of hypocrites destroy him, meaning God, who only judges of, and punishes hypocrites. Others would have the word God, to be understood without any such including or reference. See on Chap. 3:3. But it may be understood impersonally, If he be destroyed. For there was no antecedent mentioned before. See the like phrase, *This night do they require thy soul*, that is, *It shall be required*, Luke 12:20.

*{destroy}* Heb. *swallow up*. And secondarily it signifies for to destroy; for things swallowed up are destroyed. And as it argues a total, so likewise a speedy destruction, as an hungry man swallows his meat without chewing, Isa. 28:7. Psalm. 35:25.

*{him}* The hypocr*it*e compared to the green tree. Or, It; that is, the tree itself; like to the destruction whereof, is that of the hypocrite.

*{from his place}* The tree out of his garden, ver. 16. The hypocrite out of his habitation and possessions, Chap. 27:21.

*{then it shall deny him}* The place where it grew shall deny him. An elegant metaphor from living creatures; as Chap. 1:19. Or *from men that will not know their friends in adversity*. He saw his place in his prosperity, ver. 17. but it will take no notice of him in his destruction. It shall deny that ever any such tree grew there, it has so clean forgotten it. This argues a destruction of root and branch, so that no sign remains of any such tree ever growing in that place. So the men of the place shall as clean forget the hypocrite after his destruction, as if he had never dwelt among them. See on Chap. 7:10. The word כחש signifies. 1. To deny, Gen. 18:15. 2. To fail, Hos. 9:2. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{saying, I have not seen thee}* He brings in the place itself speaking to the tree for the greater assurance, and emphasis, Psal. 37:35,36. Or, *I see thee not*. You have been here, but I know not what is become of you. So the preterperfect tense is translated by the present tense thrice, Psal. 1:1. For See, look on Chap. 19:27. on Behold.

**V. 19. ***{Behold this is the joy of his way, and out of the earth shall others grow}* Here he applies the former similitude of the tree, set out vers. 16,17,18.

*{Behold}* This word as in Scripture it is used to set out some great unusual matter worth the noting; so it is also put before ironical speeches, that the earnestness of them may the more be observed; as Gen. 3:22. For Behold, see on Chap. 19:7.

*{this is}* All his joy is come to this; it ends in destruction. No doubt he strikes at Job's losses herein, whom he accounted an hypocrite.

*{the joy}* This is the joy, in which he pleased himself in his greatness. Behold what is become of it! Some conceive the word imports a great inward joy, and so great, that it cannot be kept in, but will be showed forth by leaping, trampling, and such like outward expressions, Chap. 3:22. An allusion to dogs, who leap on their masters for joy to welcome them home. Here is an end of all the joy that an hypocrite has: and what good has he by his former joy, when he is destroyed? Luk. 12:19,20.

*{of his way}* Not of his conversation, as the word is often used in Scripture, but of his state or condition: as Psal. 1:6. So paths signifies, ver. 13. For Way, see on Chap. 17:9.

*{out of the earth}* Heb. out of the dust. Out of them that have been undone, or are very poor, Psal. 113:7. 1 Sam. 2:8. Psal. 107:41. For Dust, see on Chap. 14:8.

*{others}* He and his posterity shall be utterly destroyed. He shall leave none of his to be his heir, but a stranger shall inherit all his wealth, Chap. 27:16,17. Eccl. 4:8. As other trees grow where some are cut down, so other families shall as it were spring out of the earth, and live where he did, and shall enjoy his possessions.

*{shall grow}* As plants or trees do, for so the word signifies: and he goes on elegantly with the similitude. Others read it, *And out of another land shall they grow*. To wit, who shall inherit his possessions. They shall come out of remote places, to show the more clearly, that his subversion, and the disposition of his state afterwards is from God. So trees and flowers are brought out of other countries, and thrive here, to show, that it is God, and not the earth, that makes them to grow.

**V. 20. ***{Behold God will not cast away a perfect man, neither will he help the evil doers}* Hitherto were the words of the ancients repeated by Bildad, from ver. 11. to instruct Job. Now Bildad concludes from thence in his own words, that the estate of the godly is happy, and the condition of the wicked helpless. From those things which the elders had taught him, he infers that which he had formerly proposed to himself to prove; to wit, that though Job had impatiently fretted against God, yet God was just, and loved the righteous, but hated the wicked. And leaves it to him to apply to himself; intimating that if he repented, God would raise him to prosperity again; if not, he must look for no help from God.

*{Behold}* Lo I have clearly proved, that which I first propounded, that God will not fail good men, nor help bad. See on ver. 18. & Chap. 1:12.

*{God}* A name taken from strength. God will not use his power to crush them that are good, nor to raise them that are bad. See on ver. 3.

*{will not cast away}* The word signifies to despise, as Chap. 19:18. to loathe, as Chap. 7:16. to refuse, as Chap. 34:33. And as a thing despised, yea loathed, not only to refuse it, but also to cast it away. But here is more intended, to wit, that God will not suffer a good man to perish for want of his help, as appears by that which follows; he will not help the wicked out of trouble.

*{a perfect man}* A sincere-hearted upright man; for no man on earth is perfect in the strict sense of the word. All have their failings, even the best. See on Chap. 1:1.

*{neither will he help}* Heb. *lay hold upon the hand, or, take by the hand*. To hold them up, when they are falling into misery, or to lift them up, when they are fallen into it. But he will do it to good men, and such as return to him, ver. 6. Gen. 19:16. Isa. 41:13. & 42:6. Psal. 73:23. See on Chap. 1:21. The sense is well expressed in the word, Helping. For Hand, see on Chap. 15:23.

*{the evil doers}* God does not refuse to help every one that does evil, for then he should help no man; but such as professedly, and studiously do evil, that make a trade or work of it, 1 Joh. 3:8,9. wicked men, ver. 22. These words, Neither will he help the evil doers, should be in a parenthesis, if it be meant of all wicked men, as seems by the opposition to a perfect man; for the words following give a reason of the former part of this verse; and not of the latter, unless by evil doers, be meant Job's enemies, who sought to destroy him, as ver. 22.

**V. 21. ***{Till he fill thy mouth with laughter, and thy lips with rejoicing}* He speaks to the perfect man, whom he mentioned vers. 20. for more assurance of God's blessing him. Or he turns his speech to Job in particular, as if he had said, I speak this to you: if you turn to him, as ver. 5,6. he will not leave you till he fill your mouth, &c. Or it may be knit to the former verse, thus; Apply to yourself what I say, that God will help good men, and assure yourself, that if you be one of those perfect ones, God will bring you out of your troubles, and not leave you till he have given you exceeding great cause of joy.

*{Till}* Not after neither. It is not exclusive. God will not give his people cause of joy, and then leave them, but give them cause of perpetual joy. So the word Till, is used, Gen. 28:15. Psal. 110:1. Matth. 1:25. If God would bring him out of his misery to abundance of joy, questionless he would not leave him after.

*{he}* God who gives his people joy; mentioned vers. 20.

*{fill}* Give you abundance of matter of joy, Psal. 81:10. & 126:2. Phil. 1:11. Col. 1:9. 1 Joh. 1:4.

*{thy}* He applies to Job in particular, what he had said in general, ver. 20. You may be afflicted for a while; but if you return, God will fully restore you.

*{mouth}* You shall have so great cause of joy, that your heart cannot conceal it, but it will break out at your mouth, and appear in your words, For Mouth, see on Chap. 15:5.

*{with laughter}* With matter to laugh at, and to rejoice; for the mouth does not laugh, but the face. The mouth utters our joy in speaking; the face in laughing, Gen. 17:17. and 21:6.

*{and thy lips}* The lips are the instruments of uttering matter of joy, as well as the mouth. For Lips, see on Chap. 15:6.

*{with rejoicing}* Heb. *shouting for joy*. With loud sounds of joy, and cheerful acclamations. So the word signifies, 2 Chr. 15:14. Psal. 89:15. & 100:1. Such a shout as conquerors make, when the enemies fly, or their walls fall, Josh. 6:5. Exod. 32:18. Or, as people make at the coronation of a king, Num. 23:21. 1 Sam. 10:24. 1 King. 1:40. Or, as harvest men make, when the harvest is brought in, Isa. 9:3. All this shows the greatness of that prosperity, that Bildad conceived would befall Job upon his repentance.

**V. 22. ***{They that hate thee shall be clothed with shame, and the dwelling place of the wicked shall come to nought}* When you shall have gotten the victory over your enemies, and are restored to your former prosperity, then they that rejoiced at your adversity before, shall be ashamed, and you shall see all ungodly men come to nothing.

*{They that hate thee}* That bear ill will to you. Or, *Till they that hate thee*. God shall heap up prosperity on you so long, till your mouth be filled with joy, and till your enemies seeing you raised beyond their expectation, shall be filled with shame. Or, *Because they that hate thee*. For that might be one cause of his joy.

*{shall be clothed}* Filled with shame, as if they were covered all over with it, as with a garment; Psal. 35:26. & 109:18,19. & 132:18. Ezek. 7:27. They shall be wonderfully ashamed, 1 Pet. 5:5. Or publicly, and visibly. Their shame shall be as apparent, as their clothes. Or, Daily shamed, as daily, as they put on their clothes every morning. See on Chap. 7:5.

*{with shame}* Opposed to laughter, vers. 21. for they who laugh, hold up their heads; but they that are ashamed, hold them down, Chap. 6:20. because they have lost their comforts, or hopes.

*{and the dwelling place}* Heb. *Tabernacle, or Tent*. See on Chap. 5:24.

*{of the wicked}* See on Chap. 3:17.

*{shall come to nought}* Heb. *shall not be*. Shall not remain any longer. It shall be quite consumed, as if it had never been, vers. 18. It shall be destroyed by some special judgment, Num. 16:19. for all men's tabernacles must decay in time. Their houses, and goods, and all they have, shall be utterly ruined.

## CHAPTER 9

**Vers. 1. ***{Then Job answered and said}* In this chapter, and the tenth, Job gives a larger answer to Bildad's oration in the former chapter, intermixing withal, divers sad complainings of God's heavy proceeding against him, and renews his suit to be eased from his miseries by death, and presents it earnestly with new arguments. For all the words of this verse, See on Chap. 16:1.

*{Then}* See on Chap. 4:1.

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1:1.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 6:1.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 3:2.

**V. 2. ***{I know it is so of a truth, but how should man be just with God?}* Job here acknowledges, that God often punishes wicked men, as Bildad had affirmed, but did so astonish him with his power, that he could not defend his own innocency before him. Others take this speech of Job in this and the following chapter, to be an humble acknowledgment of God's justice, and his own sins. But that cannot well agree with the unruly passages that follow afterwards; for in the whole book Job speaks not more against God's proceedings with him, any where than in those two chapters; and in some places so high, as can hardly be mollified by any enforced interpretation. It becomes us therefore not to confound things, but to acknowledge his sinful passions; when they appear, and his graces when they manifest themselves.

*{I know it is so of a truth}* Or, *Indeed I know it is so*. I know God does usually bless the godly, and punish the ungodly, as you say; I confirm it for a truth. The Heb. word כן signifies. 1. The foot, or lowest part of a thing, Exod. 30:28. 2. A place, or office, Gen. 40:13. 3. Right, or true, Numb. 27:7. 4. So. Isa. 55:9. So here.

*{but how should, &c.}* Or, *but how shall, or, can*. So the future is interpreted, Chap. 8:10,11. If God will afflict a righteous man, there is no way for him to clear his innocency, God is so powerful an adversary.

*{man}* The word signifies a sickly, weak, miserable creature, Chap. 5:17. & 7:17. A fit name to show his weakness, and by consequence his inability to plead with God.

*{be just}* That is, be justified or clear himself; as Psal. 143:2. & 51:4. Job 13:18. God's power will so awe him, that he cannot speak in his own cause.

*{with God}* Or, *before God*. In God's account, who will overpower him. Or, *in comparison of God*, as some would have it. But it suits not well with Job's scope, who stands too much here upon his own innocency.

**V. 3. ***{If he will contend with him, he cannot answer him one of a thousand}* Job goes on to show upon what unequal terms man must plead with God, if he desire it.

*{If he}* If man would plead his cause before God. Or, if God be pleased to call man in question.

*{will}* Heb. *delight*. Take pleasure to contest with men. So the word signifies, Numb. 14:8. If it be God's pleasure to question me, I must be condemned, be I just or no. He professedly speaks to this purpose, ver. 21,22. For Will, see on Chap. 22:3. on Pleasure. Also it signifies sometimes to move, Chap. 40:17.

*{contend}* By way of arguing. If he have a controversy against man. So the word is used for judicial pleading, Hos. 2:2. & 4:1. Mic. 6:1. Hence Gideon had his name Jerubbaal, Judg. 6:31,32. And striving with the Maker, is not by force, but by way of argument, Isa. 45:9. And that appears to be the meaning here, by that which follows. he cannot answer him.

*{with him}* God with man, or man with God.

*{he cannot answer him}* God is so powerful, that he will stop man's mouth, and not suffer him to speak for himself; as follows in ver. 4.

*{one of a thousand}* Or, *to one thing of a thousand*. If man desire to clear himself before God, he may call a thousand times for a hearing, but God will not give him audience once. Or, Man may hear many crimes objected against him by God, but shall not have liberty to purge himself from one of them by apology. Or, Though he have much to say for himself, yet God will suffer him to say nothing. God the judge will esteem man's condition so vile, that he will not vouchsafe him an answer to one word of a thousand. If man contend with him, God will not answer him. So this number is used indefinitely for a very great number; Lev. 26:8. Deut. 5:2. 30. Josh. 23:10. Others interpret this as the former verse, for a further justification of God's justice, and acknowledgment of Job's sinfulness, and inability to plead with God in point of equity. But this agrees not with what follows; where he brings not God in as a just judge, that would hear him with patience; but as one that would go beyond him in policy and power, speaking altogether of God's wisdom and might, but nothing at all of his justice. For Thousand, see on Chap. 1:3.

**V. 4. ***{He is wise in heart, and mighty in strength; who hath hardened himself against him, and hath prospered?}* God is too politic and strong for me to deal with, vers. 32,33. Therefore I cannot plead my cause before him with expectation of a good issue.

*{He is}* God; mentioned vers. 2.

*{wise in heart}* An allusion to men, whose wisdom is attributed to the heart, which is the seat of wisdom, Prov. 2:10. And the heart is put for wisdom, and understanding, Chap. 34:34. Prov. 6:32. & 19:8. Hos. 4:11. Luk. 24:25. and hearty, in the original, is translated wise, Chap. 11:12. where both these are added, it argues a fullness and excellency of knowledge above others, as if the same word were doubled, Exod. 35:25. *The wise-hearted are such as God hath filled with the spirit of wisdome*, Exod. 28:3. For Heart, see on Chap. 15:12.

*{mighty in strength}* Almighty. Therefore the word is doubled, as also Isa. 40:26. See the former note. Thus greatness of God's wisdom and strength, mentioned not so much to show the integrity of God's judgments, who wants not wisdom to judge, not power to execute his sentence; but rather to show, that God will always be too hard for man, let man plead as well as he can for himself. For Strength, see on Chap. 6:12.

*{who hath hardened himself against him}* Who ever resolved to stand it out with God, that was not overthrown, though his cause were never so just? Or, who ever pressed him hard; as 2 King. 2:10. Or, who ever opposed him in disputation. So the word is commonly taken by the Rabbins, because in disputations hard and difficult questions are produced, and arguments pressed.

*{and hath prospered}* Heb. *hath had peace*, Chap. 22:21. Whoever did it, has wrought himself perpetual trouble. By peace, here, is meant prosperity, as Chap. 8:6. Who ever got the better of God, or was not overcome by him, or brought his cause to perfection? See on Chap. 5:24. This clause also is ordinarily interpreted, as a clearing of God's justice against all opposers: But it seems rather to be an acknowledgment of his power, which kept Job from pleading with him; though he wish elsewhere that he might do it upon equal terms, vers. 34,35. and thinks God deals too rigidly with him. The word שלם signifies. 1. To be full, Gen. 15:16. 2. To have peace, or prosper, Job 22:21.

**V. 5. ***{Which removeth the mountains, and they know not: which overturneth them in his anger}* He goes on to set forth God's infinite wisdom, and power, by many particular acts of his, to show, that there is no contending with him. Better sit down with loss. He sets it out first by his acts upon unreasonable creatures in earth, sea, heaven, to the 12. verse; and afterwards upon reasonable ones, that it may appear, that neither can resist God, and prosper.

*{which}* God, spoken of, ver. 2. for these following are not acts of men.

*{removeth the mountains}* Some understand it of kings, who build palaces and forts upon mountains, as it were hills upon hills: but seeing the things following are to be literally understood, it is fit this passage should be so understood also. It likewise crosses the former distribution, which shows, that God begins to deal with reasonable creatures, at the twelfth verse. They which understand it of the hills themselves, differ. Some read it, *which can remove the mountains*. Because that which follows ver. 6. of shaking the earth out of her place, was never performed, and seems to cross Psal. 96:10. & 119:90,91. But seeing the things following have been done, the words rather show what God has done, than what he can do; and that shaking the earth out of his place, ver. 6. must be understood of a partial, not total remove. This removing of the mountains, some take to be done in their first creation, when God made hollow places in the earth to lodge the sea and waters in, and out of the rubbish cast up made the mountains, Psal. 104:8. Others take it to be done by earthquakes; And they also differ. Some think it to be by removing them from one place to another, as some have been in an earthquake, Rev. 6:14. Others, for taking them quite away; which suits well with that that follows, of overturning them. But the removing of them from one place to another, seems to be the meaning of these words; for so the Hebrew word עתק signifies, Chap. 14:18. & 18:4. Gen. 12:8. & 26:22. Isa. 28:9.

*{and they know not}* They neither could perceive it before, nor feel it, when it was doing. He compares the hills to men, who if in their sleep they should be removed into another place; would much admire at the strangeness of the act, when they awake, Hos. 7:9. Isa. 45:8. Prov. 23:35. Such metaphors are frequent in Scripture, Psal. 114:3,4. See on Job 1:19. & 6:30. So knowledge and speech are in Scripture often attributed to places, Chap. 7:10. & 28:14. Others refer it to the men that dwell in the mountains, or by them; who wonder to see in an earthquake mountains removed; and thought it a thing impossible, Psal. 30:6,7. Nah. 1:5. But the context requires it to be understood of the mountains themselves.

*{which overturneth them}* Some read it, *so that he overturneth them*: Making it one act with the former. But it is rather an aggravation of God's anger, setting forth a further proceeding of it, in that he does not only remove hills out of their places by an earthquake; but in his anger with the sinful inhabitants, does pluck them up by the roots; and sometimes burn them up with flames, and lay them equal with the valleys, as has been done in Sicily, and other parts of Italy, and lately in the Canary Islands. See Psal. 18:7,8. & 144:5. Rev. 8:8. & 16:18,20. Or, *That he overturneth them*: They wonder how it comes to pass, they know it not to be God's act.

*{in his anger}* Heb. *in his nose*. For the nose, by the high color of it, and shortness of the breath, shows anger, Chap. 4:9. As God has no face, nor nose, so neither is he subject to anger: but does as a man angry, overturn all in his way. So he repented that he made the world, when he destroyed it, Gen. 6:6,7. Such speeches set out God's effects in the world, not any subjection to passion, nor changeable affection in God. For Anger, see on Chap. 14:13. on Wrath.

**V. 6. ***{Which shaketh the earth out of her place; and the pillars thereof tremble}* He proceeds to show the greatness of God's power; by his working upon the earth itself, which is greater than the mountains, and the pillars of it, which no man can make to shake. Both are joined together; Psal. 46:2.

*{shaketh the earth}* The word imports, making to tremble. A similitude from men frighted inwardly so much, that all their joints are loosened, and members shake, Dan. 5:6. Deut. 2:25. Prov. 30:21. & 2 Sam. 22:8. Jer. 51:29. Psal. 104:32. For Earth, see on v. 24.

*{out of her place}* Out of the place she was in, Job 8:18. But this must be understood of some part of the earth, for the whole was never removed. See on ver. 5. Thus God has removed part of it, in the firm land, by earthquakes, and parted some islands from the mainland, and buried some parts of the land in the sea, Amos 9:5.

*{and the pillars thereof}* Or, *so that the pillars*. For the earthquake is the cause of the shaking and trembling of the pillars of the earth. It must needs be a very great wind, that makes not only the walls of the house, but also the pillars of it to tremble. So it is used, Job 7:20. And it is more emphatical, to show the cause of the trembling, than another distinct work upon the pillars of the earth. The word pillar is derived from עמד to stand. 1. Because they stand upright themselves, and that in the midst of storms, 1 King. 7:21. Gen. 35:20. 2 Sam. 18:18. 2. Because they uphold temples, thrones and houses, and make them to stand, 1 King. 10:12. Judg. 16:26. According to the first sense, some interpret the pillars of the earth to be the mountains, which stand above the rest of the earth like pillars, for ornament, not for upholding. But that cannot be the meaning here; for Job proceeding in commendation of God's power, and having mentioned a greater work of God upon the mountains, vers. 4. removing and overturning them, he would not now return to a less, of making them tremble. It must therefore be meant of pillars for upholding. Meaning thereby the lowest parts of the earth, which are as the pillars to uphold the rest, 1 Sam. 2:8. Psal. 75:3. So some take the pillars of heaven, mentioned, Job 26:11. to be the highest mountains, which seem to uphold the heavens. Others take it for the equal poising of the earth, whereby it seems to be upheld, and kept in his constant posture, having nothing else to support it, Psal. 104:5.

*{tremble}* They are not removed out of their place, but tremble in it, ready to cast down their load, like men affrighted, Job 21:6.

**V. 7. ***{Which commandeth the Sun, and it riseth not, and sealeth up the Stars}* God does great things, not in earth only, but in heaven also, where no man can come to work. God does what he pleases in heaven, Psal. 135:6. Dan. 4:35. As he makes the earth to move, so he makes the sun not move, and the stars to hide their heads, when he pleases.

*{commandeth}* Heb. *Speaks to*. God speaks like a king, by command, Psal. 33:9. & 147:15. Gen. 1:3. 2 Cor. 4:6. 2 Chro. 29:24. And the Ten Commandments, are called ten words, Exod. 34:28. in the margin. For Commandeth, see on Chap. 16:1. on Said.

*{the Sun}* A name taken from the heat of it, as the learned conceive.

*{it riseth not}* Some take it to be a description of the night; as that which follows is of the day. God forbids the sun to rise in the night, and the stars in the day. Others take it for an allusion to the three days' darkness in Egypt, wherein, neither sun nor star appeared, Exod. 10:21. &c. Others would have it an allusion to the sun's standing still, Josh. 10:13. and going backward, Isa. 38:8. For in the former, the sun rose not with the antipodes one morning, and the stars were sealed up part of the night; and in the latter, the sun did not appear in the other part of the world so soon as was expected. But it is likely, that both the Egyptian darkness, and the sun's standing still and going back, were after Job's time. But it is rather a description of cloudy days and nights, wherein the sun appears not by day, nor any star by night; and then it is all one to us in eclipses and fogs, as if the sun did not rise, for we see it not, nor other things by the light of it. God keeps the morning dark sometimes, as if it were still night, Amos 4:13. He sends many misty days, wherein the sun is not seen, Joel 2:2. and nights suitable, that have no stars, Ezek. 32:7. Acts 27:20. Job 3:4,5,6. Amos 5:8. & 8:9. Isa. 13:10. Hindering that the sun from rising, is, from shining in the day, as it does other days; as sealing up the stars is, hindering them from shining in dark nights. Thus God crosses the ordinary course of things when he pleases, keeping the sun and stars from giving light in their seasons; although made by him to that end, Gen. 1:14.

*{and sealeth up the stars}* Heb. *sets a seal upon the stars*. There is a twofold use of seals: one to keep things safe, that no man may meddle with them: as Dan. 6:17. Matth. 27:66. So the stars are sealed, or set fast in their orbs, where no man can make them away. Another use of a seal is, to keep things secret, that none may know them. So letters and treasures are sealed up, Job 14:17. Esth. 3:12. Isa. 29:11. Dan. 9:24. & 12:4,9. Deut. 32:34. Rev. 10:4. This is that which here is intended. God makes the stars hide their heads, sealing them up with the sunbeams in the day, or clouds, or darkness in the night; as the Chaldee paraphrase has it. He does, as it were, command them, under his hand and seal, not to shine: and this seal of God no man can open. Some understand it of those stars that never appear to us, but are as it were sealed up from us, though visible to the antipodes, 37:7.

**V. 8. ***{Which alone spreadeth out the heavens, and treadeth upon the waves of the Sea}* He searches every where for evidences of God's power, from earth unto heaven; thence down to the sea, Psal. 104:3,5,6,25.

*{which alone}* Either the sense is; None can do it but he, or he can do it without the help of any other. Both are true, and argue the infinite power of God.

*{spreadeth out}* There are divers sorts of spreading in Scripture and common speech. As by beating, gold is spread, Exod. 25:18,19. The cherubims beaten out of a lump of gold, spread their wings abroad, 1 King. 8:7. By melting; as bell metal, and looking-glasses, Job 37:18. By opening, as curtains are spread, Psal. 104:2. And things are spread sometimes for a covering, 2 Sam. 17:19. The word נטה signifies. 1. Stretching out, or spreading a thing, Exod. 6:6. 2. To decline, or go downward, Psal. 102:11. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{the heavens}* Not the third heavens, where the angels are; nor the second, where the stars are; but the first, where the clouds are: God stretches, or spreads a covering of clouds over them, when he intends to send rain. For he speaks not here of works of creation, but of providence; and heaven is put for the clouds. Compare, Psal. 68:4,8. with Dan. 7:13. Acts 1:9. Judg. 5:4. Job 36:18,29,32. Psal. 147:8. or it may be understood of spreading the air with light in the day, and darkness in the night, Job 36:30. Psal. 104:20. The word also signifies bowing down, as Psal. 18:9. and when the cloud sails all over the heaven, it seems to be bowed down. For Heavens, see on Chap. 1:16.

*{and treadeth upon}* Or, *spurneth*. Shows his power over the sea, by doing what he pleases there, though the waves seem to swell against him. As a man that walks on dry land can do what he pleases there, but not so on the waters. God only can do this. But it is rather to be understood of stilling of tempests. When the sea rages, God does as it were trample under foot the swelling waves thereof, and lay it smooth again, as if he would walk upon it, Psal. 89:9. & 93:3,4.

*{the waves of the sea}* Heb. *heights, or high places*. Waves are high, and as it were the mountains of the sea, Isa. 58:14. Amos 4:13. Psal. 107:25,26. They are lifted up by the winds. For Sea, see on Chap. 14:11.

**V. 9. ***{which maketh Arcturus, and Orion, and Pleiades, and the Chambers of the South}* Having mentioned the heavens, he goes on to speak of some principal stars, which distinguish the seasons of the year, and some of them bring tempests. The whole sky from the North Pole, which lies in our sight, to the South Pole beneath us, which is hidden from us, is decked by his power with many glittering stars, which he makes to appear at several seasons, as governors, and distinguishers of the several quarters of the year.

*{maketh}* Makes to arise at set times, and to appear in several places to do him service. So God made Moses and Aaron, 1 Sam. 12:6. and in the margin, that is according to the translation, advanced them to be the deliverers of his people out of Egypt. And as was observed before, works of providence are here spoken of, not of creation. So Chap. 38:31,32. God convinces Job of weakness, and demonstrates his own power, by regulating these stars. See the like Amos 5:8. For Makes see on Chap. 14:9. on Bring forth.

*{Arcturus}* The Hebrew word is derived from a verb, that signifies to meet together: and hereby is signified a company of stars meeting in one constellation, wherein many lesser ones are gathered about one great one, and are called Arcturus his sons, Chap. 38:32. Arcturus is a Greek word signifying the Bear's tail, so called by the astronomers, because those stars are like a Bear's tail; or as others think, are near, and behind another constellation, called the great Bear: These stars arise in the beginning of September, at which time the ancient Eastern people began the year. This was esteemed the beginning of autumn. The Heb. word עש signifies. 1. A moth, Chap. 4:19. 2. Arcturus: As here.

*{Orion}* The original word כסיל signifies a fool sometimes; and here it is applied to this star; because as fools are inconstant, and one hour in one mind, and another hour in another, so this star arising in the beginning of December, and beginning the winter, produces much variety of weather. Hence also one of the months has his name in Hebrew, Zech. 7:1. because it produces diversity of weather; which some think to be November; others, December, in which this star arises. But they (as most agree) beginning their months in the midst of ours, it may well agree with the arising of this star, and the beginning of winter. Orion is a Greek word, and comes from a verb, that signifies to stir or trouble; because this star troubles the air, and men also, by bringing alterations of weather, by his great inconstancy.

*{Pleiades}* The native signification of the Hebrew word is not to be found among the Jewish writers; But it appears to be the seven stars called Pleiades, arising in the beginning of March, and giving the beginning to the spring, because it is set here between those stars that begin the winter, and those that begin the summer; and Chap. 38:31. it is opposed to Orion, that by his frosty bands binds up the earth, and it on the contrary lets it at liberty to bring forth delicate fruits. The Greek word Pleiades here used in our translation, is taken *ἀπὸ τοῦ πλεῖν*; from a word, that signifies to sail, because after the rough winter past, in the beginning of the spring, sailors use to put forth to sea.

*{Chambers of the South}* Hereby is, some think, meant those stars, which are hidden from us, who live under the North Pole, and are seen by them, who live under the South Pole. And therefore they are called chambers, because they are hidden from us in this part of the world, as if they were lodged in secret chambers; Prov. 7:27. Judg. 15:1. Cant. 3:4. The Chaldee paraphrase reads it, *The chambers of the stars*, the constellation of the planets in the South part. And that he names not those stars, because they are hidden from us, though they appear to other parts of the world, as the former do to us. But it may probably be deemed, that he rather means those stars that arise about the beginning of summer, in the heat of the year, as if they came then out of certain Southern chambers, or closets; as the Dog-star, and others. He intimates that God orders all seasons, and motions of the stars, including the rest under these by a figure of the part for the whole. There being the same reason of all: and that being too great a work for man to do, sets out God's omniscience, and omnipotency. This verse shows, that the godly men in those times had much skill in astronomy and astrology: though they did not abuse that skill to the predictions of future events, as some now do; but used it, to convince men of the mighty power of God, as Job does here.

**V. 10. ***{which doth great things past finding out, yea and wonders without number}* Here he breaks off the enumeration of God's wonderful works in particular, with a general conclusion; as if he had said, These are but a small part of God's wondrous works, which I have reckoned in the heaven, the earth, and the sea: there are many more about meteors, metals, and other secret things, which our eyes never beheld: Which as they are more than any man can find out, or reckon up; so when God does reveal them to us, are sufficient proofs to convince the most blockish persons, of God's eternal power and wisdom, Psal. 72:18. & 77:11,12, &c. & 86:10. Rom. 11:33. See on Chap. 5:9. out of which this verse is repeated word for word: and therefore needs no notes here.

**V. 11. ***{Lo he goeth by me, and I see him not: he passeth on also, but I perceive him not}* God is a spirit invisible, and therefore I cannot find him out to plead my cause with him, when I desire it; nor have any warning to defend myself, when he purposes to call me into question. He has all advantages against me, and therefore I must needs be cast; though I could say much for myself, if I were upon equal terms with him. Such a desire of pleading with God, Job expresses, and the impossibility of obtaining it, Chap. 23:3,4,8,9.

*{Lo}* Or, *Behold*. As it is translated, ver. 12. See on Chap. 1:12. For Lo, see on Chap. 19:7. on Behold.

*{he goeth by me}* Not that God moves from place to place, as men do; but he being spiritual, is as invisible and incomprehensible to the eye and mind of man, as a man that passes by unseen, and unthought of. He is nigh, yet I cannot come to plead with him, I cannot perceive him ready to that purpose. For the word, see Exod. 34:6. For Go, see on Chap. 15:19. on Passed.

*{and}* Or, *yet*. Though he be so near me, yet I cannot see him, for he is invisible, Acts 17:27. So this particle is translated, 2 King. 8:19,22.

*{I see him not}* Or, *I cannot see him*. For See, look on Chap. 19:27. on Behold.

*{he passeth on}* See Chap. 4:15. Or, *repasseth*: Goes forward and backward by me. He is round about me, Psal. 139:5. Acts 17:28. Heb. *He changeth*; to wit, his place.

*{perceive him not}* Or, *discern him not*; as the same word is translated, Chap. 6:30. See notes there: as also on ver. 5. of this chapter.

**V. 12. ***{Behold, he taketh away, who can hinder him? Who will say unto him, what doest thou?}* In this verse, and the next, Job shows man's great weakness, that if God should take away all he has, be it deservedly or no, yet he cannot hinder him, nor dare not ask a reason of it, nor cannot look to have help from any friend; because God's power is so great, that he would confound the stoutest, that should take part with any, whom he spoils. All tends to this purpose, that Job in this discontented passion he was now in, persuaded himself that God would overpower him, and not give him a just hearing. Not that he thought he was no sinner, or had deserved no corrections; but that he was not so great a sinner, as to be made an example of God's anger to present and future ages, by so great judgments inflicted upon him; contrary to God's proceedings in those times, wherein God used to prosper the godly, and afflict the wicked most grievously: We find the Psalmist subject to the like temptation, Psal. 73. Jer. 12:1.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1:12. Or, *If*; as Jer. 10:22. 2 Chr. 7:13. Or, if it be Behold, yet the verse may be read conditionally; as often in Scripture, where two things, one depending on another, come together; a condition is implied in the first, as Isa. 8:10. Take counsel together, and it shall come to nought: which is not an injunction to do so, but a prediction, that if they did so, it should be to no purpose, for God would hinder it. And this agrees with what follows here; for if is added in the beginning of the next verse. See the like sentences, Psal. 109:28. Mal. 1:4.

*{he taketh away}* The word signifies a sudden and violent taking away, as wild beasts or robbers do their prey, without any order of law. The word is used no where else in Scripture, but here, and Prov. 23:28. And therefore the one place must give light to the other. So Christ's coming is likened to the coming of a thief, 1 Thess. 5:2. But there is more intended here; for Job in his passion casts some aspersions, of some kind of injustice upon God. Else why does Elihu, in his long speeches, and God in his, towards the end of the book, charge Job with such a fault, and clear God's justice exceedingly? which shows, that Job had taxed God unjustly; and if any were, certainly most in this, and the next chapter. The speech is somewhat concise, and short, as Rabbi Levi observes on the place, and may be filled up thus; Behold he spoils a man when he will, and none can be found, that can hinder him from so doing. Some understand it of taking away a man's goods or possessions from him, as he had done Job's, Chap. 1:14, &c. And it may be Job might aim at that, for naturally we are too ready to repine at our own losses. Others, of taking away life, which Job might well expect in such miseries. And so the Chaldee paraphrase reads it, *Behold he will take a man out of the world, and who will there be, that can bring him back*? Job's meaning is in general; that whereas a man spoiled by others, may some way be helped, none can help those whom God will spoil.

*{who can hinder him?}* Heb. *who can make him to turn back*? or, aside; to wit, from spoiling: or, who can return it? or, bring it back, Gen. 14:16. & 42:25. Meaning that which God has taken away. There is no dealing with God, nor hope of restitution; though he do deprive us of what we enjoy; yea, though it were causelessly: as Job complains God dealt with him, ver. 17. For Hinder see on Chap. 15:13. on Turne.

*{who will say unto him}* Who dare ask him a reason of his so doing? Or, Who can compel him to give one? He is too strong to be questioned for any thing. For Say see on chap. 16:1.

*{What dost thou?}* Or, *why dost thou so*? Or, *why dost thou do it*? as Chap. 15:12. & 19:28. See the like phrase, 2 Sam. 16:10. Isa. 45:9. Rom. 9:20. For Dost see on Chap. 14:9. on Bring forth.

**V. 13. ***{If God will not withdraw his anger, the proud helpers do stoop under him}* If a man had never so strong help from others, yet all this cannot help him, if God's anger continue: which is so fierce, that it will ruin not only them, but their helpers.

*{If}* Some read it *without*; If, God will not withdraw, &c. and then the sense is; As no man can compel God to give an account of his anger, ver. 12. so none can hinder him from executing his wrath: He is resolved not to be hindered by any creature.

*{God}* See on Chap. 4:9.
