*{withdraw}* Keep back his anger. Heb. *Turn away, or, turn back*; ver. 12. None can keep God from executing his wrath, unless he please to withdraw it himself. He will never do it for fear of any man, nor be forced to it by the power of any creature, Chap. 23:13,14. Psal. 78:38. Prov. 15:1.

*{his anger}* See on Chap. 4:9. By anger, is meant the afflictions he lays on men in his anger, Psal. 39:10. For Anger see on Chap. 14:13. on wrath.

*{The proud helpers}* Heb. *the helpers of pride*. Or, *strong helpers*. Those who in the pride of their hearts think themselves so strong, that they can help others, yet cannot keep themselves from falling. Hence Egypt had the name of Rahab, because the Egyptians trusted in their strength, and encouraged others to trust in them, yet were destroyed themselves, Isa. 31:1,2,3. Psal. 87:4. & 89:10. Isa. 51:9. Ezek. 29:6,7,16.

*{stoop under him}* Are bowed down, and fall under the weight of God's anger, which they are not able to bear. Or, *under it*. That is, under God's wrath, They have taken too great a load on their shoulders; which must needs crush them. Thus an utter destruction in Scripture is set out, under the similitude of one falling under a burden irrecoverably, Psal. 38:4,5,6. Hab. 3:6. Judg. 27. Others interpret these three last verses, as if Job spake reverently of God's works, which none could sufficiently understand, or resist, and therefore he would humble himself before him. But what makes all this to Job's scope, who shows less humility no where, nor speaks more strangely of God's proceedings with him any where, than in this chapter? Could he speak well and ill of God's dealing with him at the same time? Jam. 3:10,11.

**V. 14. ***{How much less shall I answer him, and choose out my words to reason with him?}* If men in strength, and strongly befriended cannot deal with him, how should I do it, that am weak and friendless? Either he would not let me plead my cause, or would not be moved by the choicest words I could speak.

*{How much less shall I answer him?}* Or, *Can I answer him*; as Chap. 8:11. I cannot have liberty to answer him, and to plead for myself, although I could say much for myself. Or, *if I should be called in question by him, and suffered to speak, his power would so daunt me, that though I were never so well prepared, yet I could not speak one word for myself*. For the word Answer, see on ver. 3. For Answer see on Chap. 16:1.

*{and choose out my words}* I could use choice words for myself, if I might be heard to speak; but that liberty is denied me, Chap. 13:3. For the word choose, see Exod. 17:9. 2 Sam. 6:1. For Words see on Chap. 15:3. on Talke. And on Chap. 19:28. on Matter.

*{to reason with him}* Or, *against him*. So עם is used, Deut. 9:7. Psal. 94:16. Prov. 30:31. To answer his objections against me, or to urge my arguments against his proceedings with me. Broughton reads it, Much less can I answer him: can I wish to have pleading against him? I am so discouraged with consideration of his great power and policy, that though I could desire liberty to plead my cause against him, yet I know not how to wish it, for I am sure to be overborne.

**V. 15. ***{Whom though I were righteous, yet would I not answer, but I would make supplication to my Judge}* Or, *Whom though I be righteous, I may not answer, but must make supplication to my Judge*. I cannot have liberty to answer for myself, though my cause be just; but must be forced to sue to him, as a judge that has power over me; and will not hear me. This appears to be the sense by the following verse; wherein Job shows no will to pray to God, or hope of being heard any more; and gives a reason of this distrustfulness; ver. 17. because God causelessly afflicted him more and more, and what hope could he have then, that God would hear him? Others interpret this verse and the former, as Job's acknowledgment of his sins, and desire to be so punished, and of God's justice in afflicting him, and that he had no reason nor desire to plead with God, though he were innocent, but would rather in all humility seek to him for favor. But it were a very sudden change in Job to be so ready to pray in one verse, and so averse in the next; to lie so low before God in this verse, and to fly so high against his proceedings in the next save one. Especially Job going on in a continued speech, and not as in some Psalms mentioning different carriages, some savoring of fear in trouble, and some of joy in deliverance, because being composed after the deliverance, they mention the Psalmist's carriage both in and after trouble.

*{whom}* Or, who. It may have relation either to Job, or God, both being mentioned in the former verse, I would answer any other but him: Or, *Though I were righteous, I would not answer*.

*{though}* An usual manner of reasoning in Scripture upon supposition, as 1 Cor. 13:1,2,3. yet here may be no supposition, as was noted before, if it be read, *Though I be righteous*. This good opinion of himself, Elihu charges Job with, Chap. 32:2. and God seconds it, Chap. 40:8.

*{I were righteous}* I had a just cause to defend. Or, *I be righteous*. My cause is just, yet I cannot come to plead it. You think I have an unjust cause in hand, and have deserved to be afflicted more than other men. But I know the contrary. So the preter tense in the original is thrice translated in the present time, Psal. 1:1. That walks not, nor stands, nor sits.

*{yet would I not answer}* I dare not speak before him, being terrified by his majesty. Or, *I may not answer*. God will give me no hearing. See on ver. 3.

*{I would make supplication}* Or, *I must, &c*. So great is his force, power, and authority over me, that I must be forced to stoop to him, or perish. Though I be right in this cause, yet I may not be suffered to plead my right. For the word making supplication, or, beseeching, that it signifies earnest seeking for favor, see Hos. 12:4. Gen. 42:21. But I would make supplication. Or, *Nor would I (or, might I) make supplication*. Taking the negative out of the former part of the verse; as Psal. 1:5. I dare not so much as sue for favor, so great is his majesty: much less look for a full hearing. Rabbi Levi reads it by way of question; *Should I make supplication to my judge?* No. I should have a fair hearing. Or, I have no hope to find favor with him any more, as vers. 16. therefore I will not pray to him for it.

*{to my Judge}* To God, who is my judge; for he is the judge of all the world, Gen. 18:25.

**V. 16. ***{If I had called, and he had answered me, yet would I not believe that he had hearkened unto my voice}* Or, as it is word for word in the original, without altering tenses, as this translation does, *If I have called, and he hath answered me, I will not believe that he will hearken to my voyce.* I have had favor with God heretofore, and he has heard me, and granted my request, but he lays so great load on me now, that I am out of hope of being heard hereafter by him. This agrees with the verse following. What hope can I have, that he will hear me, that lays such a load of troubles on me? Or, according to the former translation, Though God should answer my prayers now, yet I am so terrified with my pains, and his majesty, that I cannot perceive it. For after my prayer, I am in as bad case as before, and seem to have a repulse from God.

*{If}* Or, *Though*; as it is translated vers. 15. and Amos 5:22. Hos. 4:15. I confess I have been in favor with God, and had many a petition granted, but I am out of hope of having any more. Here the particle, that often signifies a supposition, that may be false, intimates a true position.

*{had called}* Had prayed to him; as Psal. 50:15. For Call see on Chap. 15:22.

*{had answered me}* Granted my request. So answering is expounded, Gen. 35:3. Psal. 118:5.

*{believe}* The word signifies to be established; and then to believe, because faith must lean upon sure grounds out of God's word, and then it will establish the heart.

*{that he had hearkened}* Lovingly attended to what I had to say, as they do, that are willing to grant petitions.

*{to my voice}* To my prayer, that I not only conceived in my heart, but also in the extremity of my grief could not conceal from uttering with my tongue. Others read it thus; *If I cry, will he answer me? I cannot believe that he will give ear unto my voyce.* Understanding the whole verse of such prayers as he should now put up unto God. Others understand by calling, challenging God, or provoking, calling upon him for a day of hearing: and they understand Job's meaning thus; If I should require of God, and obtain a time, in which I might plead with him, yet I cannot believe, that he will pass sentence on my side, how justly soever I plead for myself. And this interpretation suits with Job's scope in these two chapters, as appears by his many complaints of God's harsh dealing with him, and desire to plead with God, vers. 18,19, &c. For Voyce see on Chap. 15:21. on Sound.

**V. 17. ***{For he breaketh me with a tempest, and multiplyeth my wounds without cause}* Here it appears, by Job's accusing God of too much severity against him, that he did not speak reverently of God before, as many commentators conceive he did. For there can be no reason to think that he intends to humble himself before God, where he complains so vehemently of him.

*{For}* As it is translated, Dan. 3:24. Or, *Because*; as 2 Chron. 15:16. The cause, why I think God will hear me no more, is because he afflicts me so heavily. Or, *who breaketh me, &c*. With what heart can I pray to him, who goes about to ruin me?

*{he breaketh me}* Or, *bruiseth me*; as Gen. 3:15. And wounds often come with bruising. Others read it, *He would break me*. But Job speaks of his present pains, not of future ones, which he feared not, who wished for death. Unless he mean, I should live in a worse condition, than now I am; if I should pray for mercy, or ask for justice.

*{with a tempest}* That is, as with a tempest; for Job was not now in a storm. So the note of similitude is omitted, Chap. 7:7. He uses two hyperbolical expressions, one taken from a tempest, the other from wounds given in war, to signify the greatness of his outward sores, and inward sorrows, arising from them, which came so thick and violently on him, as a tempest threatening to overthrow him, Josh. 10:11. 1 Sam. 7:10. Psal. 83:15. Isa. 25:4. Jer. 23:19. Ezek. 13:11,13,14. Amos 1:14. Nah. 1:3. This Hebrew word שערה signifies. 1. A storm, or tempest. Nah. 1:3. 2. Hair. chap. 4:15. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{multiplyeth}* Inflicts many wounds on me. Not wounded me in one or two places, but heaped blows upon blows, till he have left no sound part in me, Psal. 38:3,5.

*{my wounds}* My sores and sorrows; which are inflicted on me by God, as wounds are by an enemy in battle, till the blood issue.

*{without cause}* I having not committed any such great wickedness, for which so great plagues should be inflicted upon me above other men. See on Chap. 1:9. & 2:3. Psal. 35:7. Some, who would excuse this passionate speech of Job, interpret it, without showing me the cause. But the word never so signifies. Neither can these be made the words of one humbling himself before God, (as they interpret them) but of one in a great distemper, unless they be violently drawn from their ordinary construction. For Without cause see on Chap. 1:9. on Nought.

**V. 18. ***{He will not suffer me to take my breath; but filleth me with bitterness}* I have not only many and great afflictions, but also continual ones without intermission, Chap. 7:19. I am so far from a period, that I have no pause of my troubles.

*{not suffer me}* Heb. *not give me*. That is, not give me leave. For Suffer see on Chap. 14:4. on Bring.

*{to take}* Heb. *to make to return*. For breath must be drawn back, as well as put forth. He will not spare me a breathing while. For Take see on Chap. 15:13. on Turnest.

*{my breath}* So it is rendered, Chap. 19:17. I have no respite, nor lightening of my pain between whiles, Chap. 7:4. but I am filled with plagues, as if God meant quite to overthrow me. For Breath see on Chap. 15:2. on Vaine.

*{but}* As it is used, Numb. 27:3. Deut. 29:13. He is so far from giving me any time of rest, that he continually adds abundance of new troubles to the old. Or, *For he, &c*. as Deut. 29:16. And then it gives a reason of the former part of the verse. The reason why I can scarce fetch my breath, is the daily access of new troubles.

*{filleth me}* Heb. *satisfieth me*. Not that his troubles gave him any satisfaction or content, but to intimate, that he was as full of troubles, as men are of good things, that are fully satisfied with them. He had abundance of afflictions, Chap. 8:21. Prov. 14:14. The Hebrew word השביע when spoken of good things, signifies to satiate. Psal. 107:9. when of bad things, to be filled, Lam. 3:15.

*{with bitterness}* Heb. *bitternesses*. That is, with many and great plagues, which are as displeasing to the soul, as bitter things are to the palate. He had most bitter troubles, that is, extreme afflictions, Chap. 3:20. 1 Sam. 15:32. Ruth 1:20. 2 Sam. 2:26. As sweetness is used to signify pleasant things, wherein nature delights; (Hos. 9:4. They shall not be pleasing to him. Heb. *sweet*, Gen. 8:21.) so bitterness, to set out displeasing things, as troubles and sorrows; as by gall, Deut. 32:32,33. and wormwood, Lam. 3:15.

**V. 19. ***{If I speak of strength, lo he is strong; and if of judgment, who shall set me a time to plead?}* If I shall betake myself to force, questionless God is too strong for me; and if to law, who shall make him to appear, or bring him to his answer? He is every way too hard for me. I cannot deal with him by might, nor by right.

*{If I speak of strength}* Or. If I have recourse to strength. If I think to right myself that way, I shall be deceived. For Strength see on Chap. 6:11.

*{lo}* Or, *behold*. See on Chap. 1:12.

*{he is strong}* Strongest of all, and far too strong for me or any creature. We are all weaklings to him. See on vers. 4.

*{if of judgment}* Or, *if I have recourse to judgement*; as before. By judgment, is meant a course of law, wherein right may prevail against might, as appears by the opposite in the beginning of the verse. For Judgement see on chap. 22:4.

*{who shall set me}* God will not let me have any time to question his proceedings, nor no man can force him to give an account thereof.

*{a time to plead}* Heb. *who shall make me to come together with him*? Who can appoint a time or place for us to meet in judgment, and tie God to hold it? vers. 32,33. For the word signifies both appointing places and times; as appears by מועד, derived from it, which signifies both the set feasts and meeting places, as Chap. 30:23. 2 Sam. 20:5. Lam. 2:6. & 1:4. Exod. 27:21. & 40:22. And both are needful in judicature, that the persons dissenting may know when, and where to meet. So this word is translated, Jer. 49:19. who will appoint me the time? And in the margin, who will convent me in judgement? Or, *to plead*, as Jer. 50:44. in the margin.

**V. 20. ***{If I justify myself, mine own mouth shall condemn me: if I say, I am perfect, it shall also prove me perverse}* Those who would here excuse Job's passion, offer a double violence to the original words. First they translate אצדק, I justifie my self. Which is not found in that form so translated any where; and the learned know, that there is in the Hebrew a peculiar form for such expressions, called *Hithpael*. Secondly, they add I say. Whereas the words run plainly thus; Though I be just, mine own mouth shall condemn me: though I be perfect, it shall also pronounce me perverse; That is, If I should prevail to have an hearing, God would so overawe me, that I should be forced to condemn myself. This agrees well with the verse following, and with Job's scope, who though he thought himself not free altogether from sins, yet thought he was afflicted more than he deserved, and is therefore justly reproved both by Elihu, and by God afterwards.

*{If}* Or, *Though*; as vers. 15,16,21. Chap. 8:4.

*{I justify myself}* Heb. *I be just*. So it is translated vers. 15. Though I were righteous. See there. Though I have a just cause to plead, as I conceive I have.

*{mine own mouth}* I shall be so terrified with God's presence, that I shall falter in my speeches, though my cause be just, and I come never so well prepared, and he will pick matter out of mine own words to condemn me. Or, God will ask me so many questions, that he will find matter against me, though there be no witness to accuse me. For Mouth see on Chap. 15:5.

*{shall condemn me}* Heb. *shall make me wicked*. That is, shall pronounce me so; as to make just in the original, is to pronounce just, Prov. 17:15. for indeed not the fact not proved, nor the accusation makes a man wicked in the eye of the law, but the sentence of the judge. For Condemn see on Chap. 34:29. on Make trouble.

*{If I say, I am perfect}* If, Or, *Though, I be perfect*; That is, upright. They are used promiscuously, Chap. 1:1. If my cause be just, as I think it is.

*{it shall}* My mouth, as before. Or, *He shall*. God shall, spoken of vers. 19. God is resolved to condemn me, say I what I can for myself.

*{also}* As Chap. 32:10. Or, *even*, Chap. 11:18,19. Not barely condemn me, but of a grievous crime.

*{prove me}* Or, *pronounce me*.

*{perverse}* One that walks in crooked paths, Isa. 59:8. Mic. 3:9. A very ungodly man. God can make me to say I am so, though I be not.

**V. 21. ***{Though I were perfect, yet would I not know my soule, I would despise my life}* Or, *Though I be perfect, I will not know my soul, I will despise my life*. Or, *I am perfect*; should I not know my soul? should I despise my life? I would rather deny mine own innocency, and quit away my life, than come to plead with God, whose greatness would so affright me, that I should not be mine own man. Or, whatever you my friends think of me, I am upright. I think I should know my state better than you do, for I know mine own heart, and you know it not. Would you have me confess myself wicked, that so God might justly cut me off for my lewdness? I will not make so light account of my life. It is likely these are the words that Elihu fastens on, Chap. 33:9. though he misunderstand them, as if Job had denied, that there were any sin in him at all. Yet was Job justly to be blamed, as denying any such sin in him that should deserve such exemplary judgments, not considering that though he were not so wicked as others, yet his sins deserved worse plagues, even hell itself, and therefore God did him no wrong in inflicting these, which were lesser. Two things Job propounds here, the defense of his uprightness, which he prosecutes in this chapter; and his desire of death, which he urges in the next. As if he had said, I am free from these evils you object to me, my conscience acquits me of them, yet nevertheless I am compassed about with so many troubles, that I am weary of my life.

*{Though I were}* Or, *Though I be*.

*{perfect}* See on Chap. 1:1.

*{not know my soul}* Not take notice of mine own innocency, nor be ever the more confident of my cause, Chap. 10:15. Or, *not know my self, or, my life*. As if I were brought to nothing through the fear of God's majesty, and well nigh struck dead. So the word is used, Gen. 19:17. 1 King. 19:4. Jer. 51:14. For Soul see on Chap. 14:22.

*{I would despise my life}* Or, *loath it*. See Chap. 7:16. I should be weary of my life for fear of him. Or, I had rather die, than come to contend with God in judgment. See such comparisons included in absolute speeches, Psal. 40:6. & 51:16,17. Hos. 6:6. where God does not simply forbid sacrifices, (though the words seem to run so) but lay them low before knowledge, and obedience.

**V. 22. ***{This is one thing, therefore I said it: he destroyeth the perfect, and the wicked}* The consideration of what I have said before, and what I have felt myself, does evidently prove, that God shows no more favor to good men, than to bad.

*{This is one thing}* One remarkable thing in the world. Or, one thing that I purpose to maintain against you. Or, this is uniform. It is God's way of proceeding. Or, this case of mine is a singular case. God has not so afflicted any other man that fears him. Or, all I have said, tends to this one end. Or, all is one, whether I be just or unjust, I must be punished. Or, this is one thing that troubles me, that God punishes me that am upright, like a guilty person. They that excuse Job here, interpret it thus; In that which I have spoken before of God's power, I consent with you; but in that which follows, I dissent, and affirm, that God deals alike with good and bad in this world. But this will not stand with the construction of the words, unless they be forced. The words refer to what was said before, and infer a conclusion out of them; as appears by the word therefore. Instead whereof he would have said, but; if he had intended to begin a new matter, wherein he dissented from them. It is therefore a conclusion on the former premises, which Job further illustrates afterwards, to show that God deals as hardly with him, as with ungodly men.

*{therefore}* Because I think so, and have found it by observation, and am like to feel it by experience, if these judgments continue but a while on me.

*{I said}* Or, *I say*. Now, and will not hide my mind from you, but constantly affirm it for a truth. For it appears not, where Job had said so before. For Say see on Chap. 16:1.

*{he destroyeth}* Heb. *consumeth*. He not only lays pains on them, but makes an end of them. For Destroy see on Chap. 36:11. on Spend.

*{the perfect}* See on Chap. 1:1.

*{and}* As well the one, as the other; or no less the good, than the bad.

*{the wicked}* See Chap. 3:17. & 8:22. Isa. 48:22. & 57:20,21. Some writers mollify Job's speech, as if he intended only to show, that it was no argument his friends used, that he was wicked, because God so afflicted him, for he afflicts good men as much as bad: as Eccl. 9:1,2,3. Mal. 3:14. Ezek. 21:3. But that interpretation, agrees not well with those phrases of destroying the perfect, in this verse, and laughing at it in next verse. Out of which it appears, that Job in his distempered passion, goes higher than his friends, and complains of God. And from hence some conceive, that Elihu gathered that accusation against him, that he had said, He found no profit in piety, Chap. 35:3. because here in this verse, he charges God to deal as harshly with pious men, as with impious.

**V. 23. ***{If the scourge slay suddenly, he will laugh at the trial of the innocent}* When troubles come abroad in the world, God is so far from exempting, or upholding good men, that he takes pleasure to see them fall among the rest. Some writers conceive this sentence to be one of those speeches, which Job professes his sorrowfulness for, and shame that ever he uttered them, Chap. 40:3. and the other to be that, Chap. 7:15. that his soul chose strangling; because they perceived that Job never spake more hardly and sharply of God. And that therefore he was reproved of God, in the verses following, to wit, Chap. 40:6,7. This seems to be an answer to Bildad's speech, Chap. 8:18,20. He had affirmed, that God destroyed wicked men with his judgments, but not good men. Nay, says Job, in common plagues good men find no more favor with him, than the wicked.

*{If}* Or, *when*. For he speaks not by supposition of a thing that may be done, but affirming it to be so ordinarily in public judgments. So this particle is used, Numb. 35:20. Gen. 38:9. Judg. 21:21.

*{the scourge}* It comes from a word that signifies going about, or compassing about; and signifies a common calamity that goes through a country, and compasses it, scarce leaving an house untouched; as a scourge or whip compasses a man's body round about, and as the devil compassed the earth, and went all over it, Chap. 1:7. & 2:2. By the scourge, here, may be meant, any of those common and spreading calamities, as wild beasts, the sword, the plague, the famine, which go over cities, and countries, when they arise, Isa. 10:26. & 28:15. Ezek. 14:12, &c. Job might have respect here to his own scabs, which like a whip came round about his body. The Hebrew word שוט signifies. 1. A scourge for the back, Prov. 26:3. 2. Slander, or the scourge of the tongue. Chap. 5:21. 3. Oppression, or the scourge of the purse. 1 King. 12:11. 4. War, or any calamity, the scourge of the body. Isa. 10:26. So here.

*{slay}* Heb. *Make to die*. If destructive afflictions come, for all are not such, but those forenamed are: Plague, Sword, Famine, wild beasts destroy multitudes, and the good among the bad.

*{suddenly}* Neither are all destructions sudden, but some let men linger long ere they die. God takes good men away by plagues, as well as bad, and that without the least warning given; so that it is not perceived, till the thing be done, Prov. 6:15.

*{he will laugh at}* As if he took singular pleasure in the destruction of good men; else would he free them, and let wicked men perish alone in common calamities, as becomes the judge of all the world to do, Gen. 18:25. Or, *He will deride, or scoff at*; as if he were so pleased with it that he had no compassion of them at all, Psal. 2:4. Prov. 1:26. Spoken after the manner of men; for God has no face, nor cannot laugh nor weep.

*{the trial}* Those afflictions that should but try good men, Gen. 22. 1. but do indeed destroy them with the wicked. Or, *the melting away*; that is their destruction; as it is called, verse 22. and slaying, in this verse; for things melted, perish: see Chap. 6. 14. in the margin.

*{of the innocent}* If it were of innocent persons only, or gross sinners, it were somewhat strange, that the merciful God should take pleasure in their destruction: but much more strange is it to think, what he can find to take pleasure in, in the destruction of godly men. None are quite innocent, but he means such as fear God, and are free from those gross transgressions, that bring destroying judgments; as Lot was in Sodom, and Noah in the old world.

**V. 24. ***{The earth is given into the hand of the wicked; he covereth the faces of the Judges thereof: if not, where, and who is he?}* God not only takes away some good men by sudden judgments; but also gives others over to be oppressed by ungodly tyrants, to whom he gives authority to rule in the world with all licentiousness, and that over men better than themselves, and God bears them out in it, else they could not uphold their unjust sentences, as they do. None else could uphold them against the fury of multitudes oppressed by them.

*{The earth}* The earth itself, and all earthly honors and authority in it, and power over the inhabitants of it, Psal. 24. 1. or the men of the earth; as the city is put for the citizens, Psal. 97. 1. & 87. 2. or, *the land.* For it is likely, that Job had observed such things in the place, where he lived, and better knew the condition of that place, than of other parts of the world. For *Earth* see on Chap. 3. 14.

*{given}* By God, who only can dispose of it; for in this distemper Job complains of God; as appears by the end of the verse, and chap. 12. 19. For *Given* see on Chap. 14. 4. on *Bring.*

*{into the hand}* Into the power. They rule it, as they please. See Chap. 1. 12. wicked men have so much power on earth, that men may think the oppressors have conquered the world, and subdued the oppressed to live in it. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{of the wicked}* Some understand it, of Satan, as some do the last petition of the Lord's prayer, *Deliver us from that evill one;* whose power, in the world, is set out, Eph. 2. 2. & 6. 12. 2 Cor. 4. 4. But Job speaking of outward pressures, not of spiritual temptations, intends evil men, that by their power oppressed good men; and it may be, seeing he speaks in the singular number in the original, he aims at some famous tyrant in those parts, known both to himself, and to his friends, that so he might convince them, and they have nothing to say against it.

*{He}* Some understand it of the wicked man, who, by bribes, blinds the judges, and makes them pass sentence for him, to uphold him in his cruelty, Prov. 17. 23. Eccles. 7. 7. Exod. 23. 8. Others, of the wicked prince, who blinds his judges with preferments, and makes them oppress the subjects in judgment to please him. But is it to be understood of God, and none else, as the words following declare.

*{covereth the faces of the Judges thereof}* God blinds them, by giving them over to cruelty, or permitting them to take bribes, so that they cannot discern right from wrong; but as if their faces were covered, they take no notice of oppressions to punish them, nor have no pity on the oppressed to relieve them. The Chaldee paraphrast reads, *He hides justice from the face of the judges thereof*. Or, he defends them in their unjust sentences, so that no man can question them for any wrong done in judgment. So covering or hiding is taken for defense, Gen. 20. 16. Psal. 17. 8. & 31. 20. & 91. 1. Or, He gives power to tyrants to condemn just judges, that would do right. So covering the face, is a sign of a condemned person, or one to be condemned, Esth. 7. 8. For *Face* see on Chap. 14. 20. on *Countenance.*

*{If not, where, and who is he?}* It is out of question, that this power to oppress others, and to uphold themselves in it, is given them from God. Where is any one else that could do so, or who dare undertake it? Amos 3. 6. Therefore by a vehement interrogation, he affirms it to be done by God, unless men would attribute the government of the world to some other, or to blind fortune, which were not only absurd, but also impious. You can therefore attribute these disorders to no other, but the providence of God. The plural argues a kind of admiration of the doer, and of indignation at the deed; as Gen. 27. 33. Hos. 13. 10. Or, *If not, who then doth it?* If it be not God, who then does it? where is any other in the world that can do it? So it infers a consequence, Chap. 17. 15. *where is now, or then my hope?* And Gen. 27. 33. *what shall I do now unto thee?* For in both places there is an inference from these words, foregoing. Or, *If it be not he, who is he that doth it now?* So the word is used for the present time, Isa. 22. 1. Job 19. 6, 23. It is done at this time. I can find none else to be the cause of it now but God; if you know any other, speak. A kind of challenge, arising out of Job's confidence, that it was so, and could not be disproved.

**V. 25. ***{Now my days are swifter then a post: they flee away, they see no good}* He confirms, what he had said, by his own example; and sets out the vanity of his days by a threefold similitude, out of three several elements; one taken from the earth, another from the water; and the last from the air.

*{now}* Heb. *and;* It is so with me, as well as with others; and therefore I speak not without a cause.

*{my days}* My days of prosperity: If I have had any comfortable days, they are quickly gone; but the prosperity of wicked men lasts long, Psal. 73. 4. Or, the days of my life; as Chap. 7. 1. Why should I feel so much misery, that have so little a time to live? For *Days* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{are swifter}* Heb. *lighter.* A figure of the cause for the effect; for lightness of body is the cause of swiftness, Chap. 7. 6. Isa. 19. 1. Heavy bodies move slowly. The Hebrew word קל signifies. 1. To be swift, Hab. 1. 8. 2. To be light, or lessened. Gen. 8. 8. 2. To be vile, or lightly esteemed. Job 40. 4.

*{then a post}* Heb. *one that runs.* But it is used in Scripture for one employed in public service, to carry letters, or messages, from city to city, or kingdom to kingdom, on foot or on horseback; Esth. 8. 10. Such an one must make haste. My days are swifter than his running, or riding, for they stay not, nor give me no rest; which a post has sometime, for food, or sleep. My former life replenished with so many comforts, is so suddenly passed away, that no post may, in swiftness, be compared to it: and all these things were taken from me, almost before I had any enjoyment, or sense of them.

*{they flee away,}* As one that flees away in danger of his life. He makes haste, and stays nowhere. He would be swifter than a post, if he could, because his life lies at stake. So the word is used, 1 Sam. 19. 11, 18.

*{they}* My days have seen no good. He attributes it to his days; but means it of himself, who lived in those days. He saw no good in his days. So sight is attributed to places, Chap. 8. 18.

*{see}* Or, *have seen;* that is, enjoyed, Psal. 34. 12. So, to see death, is to die, Job 8. 11. To see life, to live. Job 3. 36. For *See* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{good}* Prosperity, worldly comfort, Psal. 4. 6, 7. My days passed away so swiftly, that I could not enjoy the comfort of anything I had. And now my good days are gone, and I have none, but uncomfortable ones left, Eccles. 12. 1. He speaks as one that had forgotten all his former comforts, by reason of the pressure of his present sorrows. I have had none, or very light experiment, or sense of human felicity. A post that rides in haste, or a man that flees to save his life, may see many good things, but cannot stay to taste of them. I could not so much as look upon them, which might be done in little time. For *Good* see on Chap. 21. 13. on *Wealth.*

**V. 26. ***{They are passed away as the swift ships: as the Eagle that hasteth to the prey}* The former similitude was taken from a post, or one flying to save his life on the land: here the next is taken from a ship at sea, hasting to the haven, which is swifter than a post; and the last, from a bird flying in the air, which is conceived to move swifter than a ship. All to express what little or short content Job now thinks he has had in his life. He had had a large portion of outward prosperity; but present misery had taken away the sense and remembrance of any comfort he had in it.

*{they}* My days, with all the comforts of them.

*{are passed away}* Glide away insensibly. Heb. *are changed;* go swiftly away.

*{as}* Heb. *with.* They go away, as if they kept pace with a swift ship under sail. Hence it appears, that a ship is swifter than a post: for in the verse before he had made them swifter than a post; and here he does but equal them to a ship.

*{the swift ships}* A ship goes faster than a post: but a swift ship exceeding fast. So do our days pass away. Heb. *ships of desire.* From אבה to will, or desire. That sail so swiftly, that they seem to have a great desire to be at the place appointed. Thus sense is attributed to senseless things. See on Chap. 1. 19. Ships are said to desire, as days to see good, verse 25. and the ship thought to be broken, Jon. 1. 4. Or, whose mariners at sea desire to be at home; or merchants at home, expect their quick passage, that they may escape sea dangers, and losses, and they may make gain of their rich loading, if they come home, or to the mart in time, or before others. Or, *ships that carry choice fruits,* as the Chaldee paraphrast reads it. From אביב: which signifies fruits beginning to be ripe. Such are by ships carried in haste from one port to another, lest they should rot by the way, and the voyage be lost. Or, *Pirates ships.* From איב, which imports enmity. For pirates, are sea-enemies, and choose the lightest ships, that they may soon overtake laden ones, and having robbed them, may soon get away, lest they should be pursued. For the same reasons, thieves on the land get the swiftest horses. Or, *ships of Ebeh,* which some take to be the name of some seaport, where the swiftest ships were in use; or of a river in Arabia, running very swiftly, which though the ships there be light of themselves, yet carries them by the swift stream thereof much sooner to the haven.

*{as the Eagle}* My days are as an eagle, which in flight is conceived to be swifter than any other fowl, Hab. 1. 8.

*{that hasteth to the prey}* Heb. *to food;* or, *that flyeth upon the prey.* The eagle is swift of himself; but hunger compels him to be swifter, when he flies to his prey. The eagle mounts high, yet being quick-sighted, spies a prey below, and comes upon it suddenly, like a thunderbolt, before it can shift for itself. No eagle can seize more swiftly on her prey, than sorrows do on me. By these several similitudes of things, swifter one than another, Job sets out the quick passing away of his comforts before, or his life now, and the likelihood of a speedy approach of death, if his pains were not soon taken away.

**V. 27. ***{If I say, I will forget my complaint, I will leave off my heaviness, and comfort myself}* As my prosperity was quickly gone, so on the contrary my afflictions bide by me, and will not be shaken off. For when I determined, and set myself to bear my afflictions stoutly and patiently, and laid aside all unquietness, then new torments and fears assailed me, and made me lay aside that resolution. Or, If at some time I think to comfort myself with hope of deliverance, then my sorrows grow so great, that they dash that hope suddenly. See the like complaint, Chap. 7. 13, 14.

*{If}* Or, *when;* as Chap. 7. 4. See on verse 23. of this chapter.

*{I say}* If I think, or determine fully in my mind to complain no more, Psal. 39. 1.

*{I will forget}* I will not think any more of my sorrows to complain of them, but be as if I had forgotten my past and present misery, and had clean forgotten to complain. So this phrase is used, Psal. 137. 5. If I resolved to be very patient, and never to complain any more of God's harsh dealing with me.

*{my complaint}* Because of my affliction, and misery, Chap. 7. 11. The Hebrew word שיח signifies. 1. A bush. Job 30. 4. 2. Talk. 1 King. 18. 27. 3. Thought, or meditation. Amos 4. 13. 4. Complaint. Job 10. 1. So here.

*{I will leave off my heaviness}* Heb. *my face.* I will not only cease complaining; but also change my sad countenance into a cheerful one, 1 Sam. 1. 18. For a sad look shows the grief of the heart, Neh. 2. 1, 2. Mat. 6. 16. The face is the index of the heart, and the whole man is seen in it, as in a glass, Isa. 3. 9. Eccl. 8. 1. Hence it is, that the face is put sometimes for anger, which appears in a sudden change of the countenance, Gen. 31. 2. Ezek. 14. 8. Lev. 17. 10. Psal. 34. 16. Lam. 4. 16. See the margin there. And sometimes for sorrow and heaviness of heart, as here. For that cannot be kept in no more than anger, but will appear in the countenance. The word עזב signifies. 1. To leave, or forsake. Chap. 10. 1. 2. To fortify. Neh. 3. 8. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{And comfort myself}* Heb. *strengthen;* as Amos 5. 9. Psal. 39. 13. For sorrow weakens the spirits. He would so comfort up, and strengthen his spirits, that no complaint should come from him.

**V. 28. ***{I am afraid of all my sorrows, I know that thou wilt not hold me innocent}* When I think to be eased, my sorrows will not so be thrust out of my mind, but an army of most bitter torments make me afraid. And though you tell me, that God will not cast off the righteous, yet I am persuaded he will never heal me.

*{I am afraid}* It signifies a strong fear, that makes a man shake and tremble.

*{of all sorrows}* My pains, the causes of my sorrows. It signifies sore pains, such as women bear in travail, Gen. 3. 16. a vexing sorrow, Isa. 63. 10. when I go about to comfort myself, all such heavy things as I have felt, come into my mind, vex and grieve me; and make me afraid, that they will return on me; as Chap. 3. 25. Psal. 119. 56. and let me not have any rest or comfort, but make me break my resolution, and fall to fresh complaints, Psal. 39. 1, 3.

*{I know}* By experience. I have been so kept under already, and dealt withal as a guilty person, that I know it will be so still. And though I should not murmur, or complain; yet you are resolved to continue my affliction. Thus the word is used for experimental knowledge, Gen. 22. 12. By Abraham's offering up Isaac, God had an experiment of his fear of God. And the Greek word answerable is so used, Acts 10. 34. By the conversion of Cornelius and his, Peter saw by experience, that God would receive the Gentiles to favor. And so here Job gathered by the continuance of his troubles, that silence would not ease him, I look for no clearing from you, and this adds to my sorrows.

*{thou}* You, O God; Though he reason with Bildad, yet his mind ran still upon God, as the imposer of all his troubles upon him. Or, You, O Bildad; You think I am very impatient, and complain without a cause; but I know that you will not be persuaded to clear me by anything that I can say for myself. But it appears, that he speaks to God by the like speech, verse 31.

*{wilt not hold me innocent, or guiltless}* The word signifies to make clean, and by a metaphor, to account or pronounce clean. And, by consequence, to free from all those punishments, that light upon guilty persons; as Exod. 20. 7. 1 King. 2. 9. You will not free me from trouble; as an innocent man should be freed; but keep me under pressures still, like a guilty person. He saw no likelihood of an end, and therefore concludes there would be none; and so was afraid to comfort himself.

**V. 29. ***{If I be wicked why then labour I in vain?}* The coherence and scope of this verse, is variously delivered by interpreters. Some thus; If I were an hypocrite, or guilty of gross sins (though secret) as you conceive me to be, why should I spend breath to no purpose to maintain my innocency, or desire so earnestly to be brought to trial? No malefactor desires the assizes, that he may be convicted. And I were a vain man to stand thus in defense of my cause, if I knew myself to be a wicked man. Others thus; you are resolved to esteem me an ungodly man, and you will not be persuaded to think otherwise of me; and therefore I shall lose my labor to go about to purge myself to you, who will believe nothing that I say. Others thus; God is resolved to condemn me, and to deal with me, as with a wicked person, to lay load on me still; and therefore let me clear myself as well as I can, or cease complaining, yet God will pass sentence against me. I am persuaded by God's carriage to me, not only that I shall not be delivered, that I may appear to be godly; but that I shall be dealt withal like a wicked man, and have more troubles laid upon me: why should I then please myself with a vain hope of deliverance?

*{if}* The same particle is also added, verse 13. though it be not in the original there, nor here.

*{I be wicked}* If I be an ungodly man and fear not God, as men may think, that see me so extraordinarily afflicted. Others leave out *If,* and read it, *I shall be wicked.* When I have said what I can, or carried myself more patiently, you will not have a good opinion of me. Neither speech or silence will obtain it. Others read it, *I shall be culpable,* or, *held a wicked man;* or, *I shall be condemned:* So the Chaldee paraphrase reads it: To wit, by God, who will pronounce me so, and deal with me, as with such an one, do what I can, verse 30, 31. So the contrary word, *That thou mightest be just,* in the original, is translated, *That thou mightest be justified,* Psal. 51.

*{why then}* Heb. *why is this.* Or, *why at this time.* Why should I go against my conscience, and labor to no purpose?

*{labour I}* The word imports hard labor. Why take I so much pains to make apologies for myself, or in seeking arguments to defend my cause? Or why strive I to have audience before God? I do not long to be condemned. Or why have I labored in my prosperity to keep myself from sinful courses? as Psal. 73. 13. Or why labor I to suppress my grief and complaints? Or why do I vex myself thus at these troubles, if I have pulled them upon myself by my own wicked life? 1 Pet. 2. 20. Or, *why am I not cut off?* why bear I so much trouble in this life? The first interpretation is most natural.

*{in vain}* To no purpose. Without any hope of success, for say I what I will, or suppress my spirit as much as I can, I shall be condemned. The Chaldee paraphrase reads it, *for nothing.* See the same phrase, Psal. 73. 13, 14. and a reason like this; Because his troubles continued, notwithstanding his care of avoiding evil ways.

**V. 30. ***{If I wash myself with snow water, and make my hands never so clean}* He gives a reason why his labor to clear himself would be in vain, and sets it out by a similitude. Though I should make my cause as clean, as he that is washed with snow water, yet you will make me most foul and miserable.

*{If}* Or, *Though;* as Hos. 4. 15.

*{I wash myself}* Seek to make or declare my soul clean, by pleading my cause. An allusion to the heathen, and Jewish manner of washing their bodies after any external defilement, before they came to do service unto God.

*{with snow water}* With the clearest water like to snow. Or it may be some might use snow water in their ceremonial ablutions, as clearest water falling from heaven, and having no earth mixed with it.

*{and make my hands never so clean}* The hands in Scripture used for actions, because they are the principal instruments of acting, Psal. 18. 20. Isa. 1. 15. Jam. 4. 8. Heb. *Wash my hands in innocency.* Make them as clean as if they were washed in purity itself. Though I could justify all my actions. Others read it, *wash my hands with soap;* as Jer. 2. 22. Mal. 3. 2. For that scours more than water. It is an allusion to the custom of former times, wherein men suspected or accused of some hidden crime, did use to wash their hands to declare their innocency, Deut. 21. 6, 7. Matth. 27. 24. Psal. 26. 6. & 73. 13. Others read it, *Wash my hands in a Well.* Though I should make my innocency as plain, as a man's hands may be made clean by washing in a whole well of water.

**V. 31. ***{Yet thou shalt plunge me in the ditch, and mine own clothes shall abhor me}* Not only my labor in clearing myself will be lost, but also I shall be more odious.

*{Yet}* Heb. *Then.* When I think I have washed myself very clean.

*{shalt thou}* O God, see on verse 28.

*{plunge me}* Put me into the filth over head and ears, and stain me all over, as dyers color their cloth, Ezek. 23. 15.

*{in the ditch}* Make me all over filthy, like a man fallen into a dirty ditch. You will condemn me, verse 20. and account me as sinful, as a man is dirty, that falls into a foul kennel. Or, You will look away from me, as a man turns his face away from one that is all over filthy and loathsome. Or, You will make me all over filthy still with noisome scabs and stinking sores, though I should purge myself. Or, *in the pit;* You will not leave afflicting me, till you have killed me, and laid me low in the grave.

*{and mine own clothes shall abhor me}* Some take it literally. My clothes shall leave me like an abominable thing. I shall go naked to the grave. Or, I shall be so noisome then, that my winding sheet, if it had life, would fly away from me, as from a loathsome carcass. Or, I shall continue so sore and full of scabs while I am alive, that my clothes would fly from me if they could, and refuse to cover me, as scorning to touch such a filthy body. This shows the greatness of his miseries, so extraordinary, that the senseless creatures are brought in, as taking notice of it. So the stones are brought in as taking notice of the Jews' ingratitude, and the earth of Cain's cruelty, Luke 19. 40. Gen. 4. 10. As if Job should have said, I shall be so loathsome, that those creatures that have no sense, seem to scent and abhor my nasty condition. So the prophets, to show the height of joy or grief, call senseless creatures to take notice, Isa. 1. 2, 3. 1 King. 13. 2. Jer. 2. 12. Others take it figuratively; My friends and they that are near to me, as my clothes are to my body, such as these three are, that pretend to comfort me, shall take part with you; and say, That I suffer all this misery justly, and shall abhor me as a grievous sinner. All this elegantly shows the little hope that Job had to be acquitted by God, or eased of his pains. Thus good men in passion sometimes wrong God himself.

**V. 32. ***{For he is not a man, as I am, that I should answer him, and we should come together in judgment.}* If I were to plead with a man like myself, I could make my cause good, but being to plead with God, I cannot come off clear, because he is too strong for me, and will overpower me in judgment. I could plead with my equal, and free myself, but God's majesty will so oppress me, that it will strike me dumb, verse 4. Eccl. 6. 10. Jer. 49. 19.

*{For}* He shows the reason why he could not be righted, before God, not for want of equity, but because of God's greatness above him, and his great weakness in comparison of God.

*{He}* Now he turns his speech from God, to his friends, seeking to give them satisfaction, why he could not clear himself before God, although his cause were just. For change of the person, see Chap. 8. 18.

*{a man as I am}* A mortal weak man, fit for me to contend withal; but an immortal powerful God, able to overwhelm me, Numb. 23. 16. For *Man* see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{that I should}* Or, *then I could.* I want not matter, but power to plead. And so it should appear, if we stood upon equal terms.

*{answer him}* In judgment, or in a judicial way, as verse 3. For he speaks not here of answering hard questions, which shows wisdom, but of answering things objected against him, which shows innocency. For *Answer* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{and we should}* Or, *and we would.* I would then be willing to maintain my cause.

*{come together}* He and I come face to face, as the plaintiff and defendant use to do. For *Come* see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{in judgment}* To plead one against another, verse 19. Chap. 8. 3. The rabbins observe, that the Hebrew word משפט, is put sometimes for pleading the cause, which is the beginning of judgment, and sometimes for the sentence pronounced, which is the middle of it; and sometimes for an authentic writing, expressing the punishment to be inflicted, and the fitness of the judge, to give power to the executioner to execute the sentence, and satisfaction to others, which was the end of the judgment in those times. Here it is taken in the first sense; for Job durst not so much as hope for liberty to plead his cause with God, much less that God would clear him, and declare as much to his friends. For *Judgment,* see on Chap. 22. 4.

**V. 33. ***{Neither is there any days-man betwixt us, that might lay his hand upon us both}* Though there be very great difference between God and me, yet if there were an equal judge before whom we might plead, that had power over us both for the time, as a judge may judge between the king and a subject, I might make my cause good; But God will judge himself, and let none else judge between us. Therefore I cannot escape, though I might have a time to plead for myself granted to me.

*{neither}* He complains, first, that God and he were not equal. Secondly, that there was none that had sufficient power to judge between them.

*{is there any days-man}* Or, *umpire.* Heb. *one that should argue, or reprove.* So the word is used, Psal. 105. 14. And so God did to Laban, Gen. 31. 24, 29, 42. One that might blame us, if we wronged one another in pleading, and might hear us argue the case with patience, and afterwards reprove or pass sentence against him that did the wrong. For *Days-man,* see on Chap. 15. 3. on *Reason.*

*{betwixt us}* That might hear us both indifferently, without inclining to either side.

*{that might lay his hand}* By his power keep us both in order. For both sides use to grow exorbitant in pleading. So that laying on the hand here, is not to strike, as Gen. 22. 12. & 37. 22. Or to bring into judgment, or imprison, as Neh. 13. 21. Luke 20. 19. Nor for blessing, as Mark 10. 16. Acts 8. 17. & 19. 6. Nor to work a miracle, as Matth. 9. 18. nor for ordination to an office, as Numb. 8. 10. 1 Tim. 4. 14. But have power to hear the cause, and decide the business. See *hand,* for *power,* Chap. 1. 12. Psal. 89. 25. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{upon us both}* Upon God, as well as upon me, that I may neither do, nor suffer wrong.

**V. 34. ***{Let him take his rod away from me, and let not his fear terrify me.}* Seeing God is so far above me, and none is above him, that may judge between us, I wish that God would give me some respite from my pain, and lay aside his majesty for a while, that I might plead my cause without fear, and then I should be freed, Chap. 6. 4. & 13. 20, 21, 22. Here Job shows plainly what he thought, in this passion, that he could clear himself, if he might be suffered to speak freely for himself, and could prove, that God dealt too hardly with him. Else why do both Elihu and God blame him for justifying himself too much, and injuring God in his speeches, which he does nowhere more plainly, than in this chapter. Yea these very words are laid in Job's dish by Elihu, Chap. 33. 6, 7. and that he here requires, is granted by God, Chap. 38. 3. & 40. 7. that Job might see, that he had done God wrong, and charged him unjustly.

*{take away}* Heb. *Remove.* It is now upon me, and hinders me from pleading my cause so freely, as I could do, if it were removed, and taken off from me.

*{his rod}* The word שבט signifies, 1. a staff cut off from a tree; or rods, wherewith men strike others, as Exod. 21. 20. 2. it is put for a scepter, for scepters were wont to be made of wood, and are like staves or rods, Gen. 49. 10. Psal. 45. 6. 3. for a tribe, which come out of one father, as many rods out of one tree, Judg. 18. 19. 4. for a dart, made of a rod with iron on the end, 2 Sam. 18. 14. 5. for a pen, which is somewhat like a rod, and it may be in old time might be made of some kind of wood as well as of iron, Judges 5. 14. 6. for a stroke given by a rod, or the wound made by it, and metaphorically for any diseases or sores inflicted by God, Psal. 89. 32. Prov. 29. 15. Mic. 6. 9. 2 Sam. 7. 14. Chap. 21. 9. & 37. 13. So it is taken here. Take away from me those sores you have inflicted on me.

*{from me}* Heb. *from upon me.* That is, from biding upon me any longer.

*{his fear}* The fear I have of him, and the dread of his great power and majesty. His formidable strength makes me afraid, Exod. 15. 16. The word signifies a very great fear or affrighting. Thence came the word *Emims,* Deut. 2. 10, 11. Giants, so called, because of their extraordinary bulk of body, which affrighted men, who thought their strength to be proportionable. So God had terrified Job with fearful apparitions, Chap. 7. 14. Beams of majesty in God make men afraid, Judg. 13. 6, 22. Gen. 15. 12. Dan. 10. 8. Let not God appear so formidable to me to make me afraid.

*{terrify me}* The word signifies a great terror, such an one as puts a man almost beside himself, 1 Sam. 16. 14. Job desires that he may not be thus terrified, because he knew that men kept in awe could not plead their own cause well. Fear hinders them. Many have lost the day being terrified by the judges, though their cause were just. This is that Job fears in this place; and this being removed, he professes, in the next verse, he both could and would speak for himself.

**V. 35. ***{Then would I speak, and not fear him: but it is not so with me.}* Now he shows what he would do, if he were freed from the former fears, and pains. He would maintain his own cause against God's proceedings.

*{then would I speak}* In my own defense to maintain my innocency.

*{and not fear him}* I would do that which now I dare not do; being overcopped with God's majesty and power, I would not be afraid to plead my cause before God. I would do it confidently.

*{but it is not so with me}* So כי is translated *but,* verse 18. And the sense is, It is far otherwise with me, for I am daunted; and kept under with sores and fears. I have not that liberty of speech. I am yet terrified. Or, *But I am not so with my self.* As it is word for word in the original. That is, Fear has put me clean out of myself, as verse 21. 34. And I am not yet come to myself, Luke 15. 17. So that I know not what I say, and therefore am no way fit to plead my own cause. Or, *For it is not so with me:* As God's scourge seems to make me, and your speeches would make others think of me. So כי is translated *for,* Chap. 33. 13. Or, *For I am not so with my self.* My conscience tells me I am not the man you take me to be. It is not inward guilt, but outward pains and affrightments, that keeps me from speaking in my own defense. If they were gone, my innocency would minister boldness to me, and you should see what I could say for myself. Some read it, *Because it is not so with me.* And knit it to the words following, as if Job had said, Seeing you will not pity me, nor God relieve me, I will no more complain to him nor you, but smother my grief in my own soul. But this interpretation crosses the division of the chapters, spoils the sense, and wrongs Job a little who complains again very bitterly in the next chapter.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1. ***{My soul is weary of my life, I will leave my complaint upon myself; I will speak in the bitterness of my soul}* The coherence depends upon the end of the former chapter. As if Job had said, I cannot get leave to plead my cause with God, and if I could, his majesty would so daunt me, that I could not speak to him: I have nothing left now in these miseries, but to ease my heart by giving free vent to my complaints, seeing God does not cease to punish me nor my friends to condemn me.

*{My soul is weary of my life}* Life is pleasant in itself, but when it grows full of troubles, it is wearisome. I loathe it, despise it, would be rid of it, (as the word imports, Ezek. 36. 31.) that so I might be rid of trouble, which lies very heavy upon me. ב is translated *of,* Deut. 3. 26. Or, *My soul is cut off while I live.* So the word is used, Chap. 8. 14. And בחיי translated, *while I live,* Psal. 146. 2. And then the sense is, My soul seems to be cut off, and divided from this filthy and stinking carcass, as if I were already dead, Psal. 88. 4, 5. Or, I cannot live long: My pains and sores will kill me. I am as good as dead already. For *Soul* see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{I will leave my complaint upon myself}* I will bear the burden of my complaint. I will suffer for it, if I complain without a cause, Chap. 13. 13. Esth. 4. 16. Or, *I will let loose my complaint concerning my self.* So some understand that place, Exod. 23. 5. that a man should not forbear to loose his neighbor's ass lying under a burden. And על *concerning,* is used, Num. 8. 22. The sense then is, I will give way to it, and not hinder my soul from complaining. Sick men and men in misery think it an ease, if they may enumerate their sorrows, and vent them to others, Chap. 7. 11. Or, *I will strengthen my complaint.* I will complain more freely, and stoutly than I did before. So the word is used for fortifying, Neh. 3. 8. Or, *I will leave my sighing for my self.* I will no longer fret inwardly; and forbear to speak out, but I will speak of all my trouble, which lies so heavy upon me. So the word is used for thought, Amos 4. 13. And על signifies *for,* 1 Chro. 16. 21. And this agrees well with what follows. I will not muse upon my misery, to make it burn inwardly, but give it a vent to ease my spirit. I will speak, &c. For *Leave,* and *complaint,* see on Chap. 9. 27.

*{I will speak in the bitterness of my soul}* In so great grief of mind, I will not be silent, but will, in words, utter all that is in my breast, Psal. 32. 3, 4, 5. It notes the cause of his sore complaint, 1 Sam. 1. 10. And the same particle is so used, to set out the cause of a thing, 2 Sam. 13. 27. Esth. 1. 10. The words that I shall speak, are not so much mine, as the words of my bitter calamity. They are extorted from me by my sorrows. See the like phrase Chap. 3. 20. & 7. 11. Exod. 1. 14. Or, *I will speak of the bitternesse of my soul.* You know not what bitter things I endure inwardly: else would you pity me. Therefore I will declare them to you. So this particle is used in the beginning of this verse, *of my life,* Or, *according to the bitternesse of my soul.* So ב is used, Lev. 25. 15. My words shall be as bitter and sharp, as my inward sorrows, and griefs are. The sum of all is, to lay down the extremity of his outward troubles, and inward perplexities, that he may make way for his following complaints of God's hard dealing with him.

**V. 2. ***{I will say unto God, Do not condemn me; shew me wherefore thou contendest with me}* Job in these words, looks upon himself, as a man condemned without trial, because he was cast into such miseries, and subject to such censures of others, as men justly condemned use to be. And knowing no cause, why he should be thus dealt withal like an hypocrite, he desires God not to use his absolute power in condemning him, but to let him know the true cause of his miseries.

*{I will say}* I am resolved to speak thus openly. I can hold it no longer, my miseries are so great. For *Say* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{unto God}* My friends condemn me; but I will try what God will say to me, who has power to judge me.

*{Do not condemn me}* Heb. *Do not make me wicked.* For the judge passing sentence on a malefactor, makes him liable to the sentence of the law, as a wicked man. Do not afflict me as an evildoer. Your dealing with me is as if I were such an one. Deal more kindly with me. See Chap. 9. 20. Deut. 25. 1. 1 King. 8. 32. Psal. 94. 22. Prov. 17. 15. Rom. 8. 34.

*{Shew me}* Heb. *Make known to me.* Make it plain to my understanding: your proceeding with me seems to be very strange: I desire to know the true ground of it: Before you condemn me, show the cause, that your judgment may appear to be just, Psal. 51. 4.

*{wherefore}* Heb. *For what.* Show me what great sin I have committed, that I should be thus heavily afflicted more than other men. Have I not feared you, and walked with a good conscience before you? yet you have let all these evils come upon me.

*{contendest with me}* I have given you no cause of so hot a contention, much less of so heavy a condemnation. The word signifies contending with a man, as an enemy in a suit, or cause. See Chap. 9. 3. Hos. 4. 1. Isa. 57. 16.

**V. 3. ***{Is it good unto thee that thou shouldest oppress? that thou shouldest despise the work of thine hands? and shine upon the counsel of the wicked?}* Do you delight to lay load on me, as these my friends do, and to justify their proceedings against me, and encourage them to wrong me? It seems by these afflictions continuing still on me, that you do countenance my friends that condemn me for an hypocrite, and those that robbed me of my goods. Thus Job stretches all the veins of his wit in an eloquent way, to express the greatness of his grief, and to draw God to a removal of his sorrows.

*{Is it good}* Good is taken sometimes for pleasing; as 2 Sam. 19. 27. & 18. 27. sometimes for profitable; as Deut. 6. 11. & 30. 5. sometimes for right and just, 2 Sam. 15. 3. Prov. 2. 9. For *Good* see on Chap. 21. 13. on *Wealth.*

*{unto thee}* Is it pleasing to you; or do you get any profit by it? or rather, seems it a just and equal thing unto you? How does this proceeding against me, agree with equity?

*{that thou shouldest oppress}* The word signifies to take away men's goods without just cause, either by fraud, as 1 Sam. 12. 3. Hos. 12. 7. or by violence, as Prov. 28. 3. Psal. 119. 122. It may be Job had respect to the taking away of his goods, Chap. 1. He speaks here, as if he thought that God did him wrong, in laying so great losses and pains upon him; as appears more plainly, verse 6, 7.

*{that thou shouldest despise}* Make light account of; as Psal. 15. 4. That you will not so much as look upon me in my afflictions, or hear my prayers. Or, *reject;* as 2 King. 17. 20. that you have clean cast me off, and let my adversaries triumph over me, and give me no assistance against them, nor deliverance out of my troubles. See Chap. 5. 19.

*{the work of thine hands}* Heb. *the labour of thy palms.* Not that God takes pains, and is weary of his work, as men are; but to show, that we are as surely made by God, as houses are by builders, that sweat and toil in the work. Though God do not labor, yet his work is made as well as if he did, and better than that on which men bestow most labor and pains. By *labor* is meant the fruit of God's work, the thing made by him, as Psal. 128. 2. Why should you despise me, whom you have made? as verse 2. God has no hands, but is spoken of here after the manner of men, who work with their hands; as Psal. 119. 73. Isa. 64. 8. See Chap. 6. 9. Builders use not to ruin what they have built. But you go about to ruin me whom you have made. This making is set out largely, verse 8. &c. The Hebrew word יגיע signifies. 1. Labor. Gen. 31. 42. 2. Goods gotten by labor, or the thing wrought. Psal. 128. 2. So here.

*{and shine upon}* Seem to favor them in their wicked counsels, and to approve of their plots against me, as if you did look cheerfully on them, to encourage them, Numb. 6. 25, 26. Psal. 67. 1. & 80. 7. Or, make their plots against me to prosper, and confirm the bad opinion my friends have of me, by continuing these great afflictions on me. So the sun by shining on the earth, brings the fruits thereof to maturity; Psal. 80. 19. & 90. 16, 17. Job 39. 34.

*{the counsel}* The advice my friends take one from another; and their joint conclusions against me, Psal. 1. 1.

*{of the wicked}* Ungodly men, such as fear not God, as my friends show they do not, by condemning me for an hypocrite, because of my sore afflictions; which would rather make those that fear God pity and comfort me.

**V. 4. ***{Hast thou eyes of flesh? or seest thou as man seeth?}* You need not afflict me thus, to find out my wickedness; for you know my heart, and secret ways, which men do not, and you know that I am not a wicked man, verse 6, 7.

*{hast thou}* Certainly you have not.

*{eyes}* Eyes and ears, and mouth, are attributed to God in Scripture after the manner of men; not that he has any bodily members, being more spiritual than the angels; but because he knows all things, and can fully reveal his will to us without these members, better than men can with them; Psal. 34. 15, 16. Josh. 9. 14. Eyes here may be put for the judging faculty of the soul. The eye being the most excellent of the senses, is put sometimes for the other senses; as for feeling, Job 3. 10. for seeing, Gen. 42. 1. and so also for the understanding and sight of the mind, which is therefore said to be opened, as the eyes are to see, Acts 16. 14. For *Eye* see on Chap. 2. 12.

*{of flesh}* Fleshly eyes. Such as men have. Bodily eyes made of flesh. God needs not the help of such eyes. He sees not as man does, 1 Sam. 16. 7. He knows the heart, and needs no further trial. Flesh is put for man in Scripture, Isa. 40. 6. Joh. 1. 14. For *Flesh* see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{or seest thou as man seeth}* Why should you continue to afflict me thus, as judges do, that know not the right, and therefore are forced, by racks and torments to bring malefactors to confession? and sometimes, out of malice, condemn and torture men, looking more on the person, than on the cause; but you know all things, and are most just. How can I then, being godly, be thus afflicted by you; as if you were a man that either knew not my righteous just cause, or would never be reconciled to me? For *See* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

**V. 5. ***{Are thy days as the days of man? are thy years as man's days?}* Have you need of much time of enquiry before you can judge, as earthly judges have? Are not you eternal, and know things past, present, and to come in a moment? You need not then spend time to enquire into me, as men do; but knowing my integrity, may clear me presently, and set me at liberty.

*{are thy days as the days of man?}* You are not of short continuance, as man is, Chap. 7. 1, 6. nor ignorant of things, as he is, till in process of time, he have learned the truth of them. Man is a miserable creature, so the word imports; as Chap. 5. 17. & 7. 17. For *Days* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{are thy years as man's days?}* God's time is set out by years, man's by days: for man continues but a while; but God lives forever. Lest therefore any man should think their continuance alike (though one might be greater than another) because in the beginning of the verse, he had attributed days to both: in the end of it, he attributes years to God, and days to man. The word for man in the beginning of the verse was אנוש. Frail, or sorrowful man. Here he changes the word into גבר. Strong powerful man. If any man should object, A sickly man's days are nothing to God's, he lives uncomfortably, and may quickly die; but a strong man's days may be something. No, neither, says Job, are the strongest man's days anything to God's years. Others interpret the whole passage thus; Are you of so short a continuance, and so full of malice and revenge, that you should seek to find out occasions to execute your spleen against me quickly, as men do, lest they should die before they be revenged. Sure it is not so with you. So David's servants persuade him to take his time for revenge upon Saul; and not to let it slip, 1 Sam. 24. 4. and Abishai does the like, 1 Sam. 26. 8. and the devil rages against the church, because of abundance of malice, and shortness of time, Rev. 12. 12. But this interpretation cannot fit well with the two verses following, which carry these words rather to enquiry of wickedness in Job, than to study of revenge.

**V. 6. ***{That thou enquirest after mine iniquity, and searchest after my sin?}* Is there any need, that you who know all things, and are not ignorant of my integrity, should torment me thus continually, to make me confess myself a wicked person?

*{that thou enquirest}* It should seem by your rough dealing with me, that you have some such purpose; for you lay one affliction upon me after another to make me confess myself guilty, as a man seeks earnestly after another man's life. So the Hebrew word signifies, Exod. 4. 19. 1 Sam. 22. 23. Jer. 50. 20.

*{after mine iniquity}* Or, *for mine iniquity;* as Psal. 84. & 85. in the titles. The word properly signifies crooked or perverse dealing, Chap. 7. 21.

*{and searchest}* As if you would make me to confess by torments, and brought me in these pains and sores as witnesses against me, and curiously and strictly proceeded against me, omitting no kind of affliction that might make me accuse myself, as judges invent new torments to find out secret treasons.

*{after my sin}* Or, *for my sin,* as before in this verse. The word signifies a lighter error, or missing of the mark, Chap. 7. 20. You do seek all advantages against me, as if you would be glad to find any little hole in my coat. So sharp is your proceeding against me.

**V. 7. ***{Thou knowest, that I am not wicked, and there is none that can deliver out of thine hand}* There is no need of torments to find out this business, for you yourself can clear me. Seeing therefore you know I am not wicked, why am I tortured, as if I were most wicked; especially seeing there is none can take off this load, you have laid upon me?

*{thou knowest}* Heb. *it is upon thy knowledge.* You know it of yourself, not by intelligence from others. See the like phrase, Hos. 10. 10.

*{that I am not wicked}* He does not say, that I have not sinned; but that I am not an ungodly man. See verse 3. Chap. 9. 29.

*{and there is none}* Or, *yet there is none;* as Psal. 119. 109, 110. It is a comfort, when a man is unjustly condemned by earthly judges, that there is a superior judge in heaven, to whom we may appeal, and he will right us. But Job sets out how hard he conceived his case to be, that was as it were condemned or punished like a wicked man by God, and so could look for no deliverance from any other. As if he had said, Why do you lay such load on me? Would you have me to sink under it? You know no man can deliver me, Chap. 9. 12.

*{that can deliver}* It signifies pulling a man out of troubles, or from enemies; as Esai. 20. 6. Prov. 6. 5. like snatching out of the fire, Jude verse 23. or out of the lion's mouth, 1 Tim. 4. 7. 1 Sam. 17. 35. None can deliver me, though you should deal never so harshly with me.

*{out of thine hand}* Out of your power; as Chap. 1. 12. 2 King. 18. 30, 33. Dan. 3. 15. Or, out of these pains, which your hand has inflicted upon me; Psal. 39. 10. 1 Sam. 6. 9. Exod. 9. 3. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

**V. 8. ***{Thine hands have made me, and fashioned me together round about, yet thou dost destroy me.}* Some give the coherence thus; You need not afflict me to find out my sins, and make me confess my wickedness, for you did make my soul, with all the faculties of it, and my body with all the members, and know what is in every one of them, Psal. 94. 9. Others thus, and more rightly, Why will you go on to afflict me, till I be destroyed, seeing you have taken so much pains in making of me? Men use to preserve their works, and not to ruin them. And so he prosecutes his argument, mentioned verse 3.

*{thine hands}* Not that God has any hands or members, but they are attributed to him after the manner of men, to show that God did as surely make us, as men do those things which they work with their own hands.

*{have made me}* Some read it, *thy hands have put me to pain.* But that agrees not well with what follows immediately, *And fashioned me.* It signifies to make a thing with much pains and labor. Not that God does weary himself in making man, but to show the excellency of the work; for such things as men take pains about, use to be accurately done. Hence comes עצב, *an idol,* because they use to be curiously made to draw men to worship them. So is man made, Psal. 139. 14, 15. as exactly, or more than those things wherein men take most pains, and show most skill.

*{and fashioned me}* Trimmed me up. Set all my parts in order, and completed me, Gen. 1. 31. Psal. 119. 73. Esay 43. 7. For *Fashioned* see on Chap. 14. 9. on *Bring forth.*

*{together round about}* At one time, and wholly. No part of me made by any other. An allusion to a potter, who makes his work by turning it round, till it be all finished, Jer. 18. 3. Or, to a statuary, who looks round about his work, to see if all be exact and complete, Job 7. 13. Luke 11. 40. Broughton reads it, *in every point:* Some join it to the words following, *yet thou wilt destroy me together round about;* That is, you will make a speedy end of me by these troubles. For *Together* see on Chap. 34. 29. on *Only.*

*{yet}* Heb. *and.* See on verse 7.

*{thou dost destroy me}* The word signifies to swallow, as greater fishes do lesser, and so they are consumed suddenly, and altogether, Chap. 2. 3. & 8. 18. & 9. 22. Psal. 52. 5. 1 Cor. 15. 54. 2 Cor. 5. 4. 2 Sam. 20. 19. Others read it, *And wilt thou destroy me?* The interrogation carries an admiration with it, as Amos 3. 4, 5, 6. Will you destroy me, as you have done my estate, though I be your creature? The former translation carries a complaint with it, as if Job thought it an unfit thing for God to deal so hastily with him, as if he would undo what he had done, Gen. 6. 7. And some observe a double emphasis in it; one in the word *yet;* the other in the word *me.* Though you have made me, yet you will not spare me, Even me, who have been careful to please you in the course of my life. How would you have dealt with me then, if I had been made by another; or had been an ungodly man? God uses the same argument to convince Jonah, Jon. 4. 10. and the church to persuade God to mercy, Esay 64. 8, 9. God made man in a better condition than the beasts, and as the principal creature on earth to whom he would do good, and planted his own image in him, and therefore it seemed strange to Job, that God should delight in the destruction of such a creature, as he seemed to do in his dealing with him.

**V. 9. ***{Remember, I beseech thee, that thou hast made me as the clay, and wilt thou bring me into dust again?}* The argument in the former verse was taken from the maker; in this verse, from the matter. Do you make me so brittle to destroy me with pains? Brittle things should be gently dealt withal.

*{Remember}* Remembrance attributed to God, who cannot forget, implies a serious thinking of what he seemed to slight before, and a speedy supply of the wants of his people, or delivering them out of trouble, Psal. 20. 3. & 132. 1. & 136. 23. & 9. 18. 1 Sam. 1. 19. Chap. 4. 7. & 7. 7.

*{I beseech thee}* Chap. 4. 7. Or, *now,* as Chap. 5. 1. for Job in his passion does not use entreaties, but complaints, as if he would say, At present you have forgotten my frail condition; else you would not deal so roughly with me.

*{that thou hast made me as the clay}* Some understand it of our creation, and conceive man's body was made of dust mingled with water, which is clay, and that here is an allusion to Gen. 2. 7. & 3. 19. But the words following seem rather to carry it to the forming of man in the womb, which he goes on to describe curiously. And it may be read, *Thou hast wrought me like clay.* As the potter tempers the clay, and works it into an earthen vessel, so does God frame and form our bodies in the womb, though not out of earth, as at first. This shows our brittle condition; we are like earthen pots quickly and easily broken, Psal. 2. 9. Rev. 2. 27. and so Job thinks it were fitter that he should be spared, because he cannot bear much more, Psal. 103. 14. Esay 45. 9. & 64. 8. Jer. 18. 4. Rom. 9. 21. For *Clay* see on Chap. 4. 19.

*{and wilt thou bring me into dust again?}* Will you by grievous torments bring me suddenly to the grave? Will you not leave afflicting me till I die? Psal. 30. 9. & 90. 3. Or, it may be read; *And that thou wilt bring me into dust again.* It will not be long ere I shall die, and therefore it were fit that I should find some rest a while before, Psal. 39. 12, 13. For *Bring again* see on Chap. 15. 23. on *Turnest.* For *Dust* see on Chap. 14. 8. on *Ground.*

**V. 10. ***{Hast thou not poured me out as milk, and curdled me like cheese?}* He goes on to describe God's great care in making him in the womb, that he may the more wonder at God's harsh dealing with him now. He sets out his first conception by a modest similitude taken from the making of cheese.

*{Hast thou not?}* Certainly you have. An interrogation negative makes a strong asseveration affirmative; as Numb. 23. 30.

*{poured me out}* As men pour out metals melted, Ezek. 22. 21, 22. So bells are made, and coin. So by God's wonderful work the seed whereof we are made in the womb is parted, and as it were poured out of the substance of our parents, as out of a vessel.

*{as milk}* As the good housewife pours out the milk to strain it to make the cheese. The seed whereof we are made, may well be compared to milk, being not much unlike it in color, as naturalists report. The word חלב signifies. 1. *Milk.* Deut. 14. 21. 2. *Fat.* Levit. 7. 2. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{curdled me}* Thickened the soft matter and liquid substance whereof I was made. The word signifies gathering together, Exod. 15. 8. Zeph. 1. 12. For moist things must be gathered closer together into a narrower room, before they can be made dry and firm; as appears in making bells, coin, cheese.
