*{like cheese}* As the woman presses the milk with her hands, and with weights, to make it cleave together, and harden, till it become curds, and afterwards cheese; so God by the natural heat of the mother, makes the seed in the womb more dry, hard, and firm, till it become a fleshly mass, and after frames it into a body. An elegant similitude to set out man's conception in the womb, and such an one as the philosophers can scarce equal. Some understand it of man's creation at the first, but that answers not to the story in Genesis, where no liquid matter is mentioned, only he is said to be made of the dust of the earth, Gen. 2. 7. and so repeated, Gen. 3. 19. But it most evidently agrees with our framing in the womb.

**V. 12. ***{Thou hast clothed me with skin and flesh, and hast fenced me with bones and sinews.}* Here Job proceeds to show what a wonderful and firm work God makes of that little and liquid matter, whereof we are made. He mentions not all the parts of man, but leaves the excellency of the inward parts to be understood by the curious working of the outward ones, which are but as a garment and fence unto them. And he follows not the order of nature, but sets out first the skin and flesh, which are more apparent to us; and then the bones and sinews, which are more hidden from us. A strange work, that out of such liquid matter should be made not only a thin skin, and firm flesh, but also hard bones, and knitting nerves.

*{thou hast clothed me}* Skin and flesh are our natural clothing to cover the inward and more noble parts, which do as it were keep state within, as not fit for the eye to look upon, and so are kept from cold as the body by the garments. And not without great need; for the brain, heart, liver, and other inward parts, being more tender than the outward, if they should lie open to all weathers, would soon ruin the whole fabric, and bring the body to the grave. The excellency of these inward parts appears, in that he accounts them to be the man, and the skin and flesh but the garment, for so he says, *Thou hast clothed me,* Matth. 6. 30.

*{with skin and flesh}* Skin is the external clothing of the inward parts; flesh, the internal, as the body is clothed with upper and under garments to keep out cold. For *Flesh,* see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{and hast fenced me}* Secured my inward parts, as a field with a fence. Heb. *hedged me,* Chap. 1. 10. The outward hedge of skin and flesh, though it might keep out cold, yet it was not strong enough to keep out all other dangers, therefore God adds an inward fence of bones and sinews, as in fenced cities, walls, and ramparts are made one within another, that if one be pierced, the other may keep out the enemy. So has God taken care for preservation of man's life, by guarding his internal parts with a double fence. Some read it, *Thou hast covered me.* For these also help to cover the inward parts, as well as flesh and skin. Others, *Thou hast sowed me together;* Which is also true. But the first reading is best.

*{with bones and sinews}* With bones for strength, and sinews for motion: and under these, as the more noble, are the gristles, muscles, and nerves, contained. Bones make the body go upright. Sinews convey sense and motion to it. The whole verse may be read interrogatively as the former, *Hast thou not clothed me, &c?* And so may the next, *Hast thou not granted me life, &c?* And the reading will go on in one phrase, and the words be more emphatic. And it is usual in Scripture to understand the sign of the question in the following sentences, having put it before the first, Job 3. 11, 20. with 23. Deut. 32. 6, 34, 37, 38. For *Bone,* see on Chap. 2. 5.

**V. 12. ***{Thou hast granted me life and favour, and thy visitation hath preserved my spirit}* He proceeds from man's formation to his quickening and preservation in life, and to the infusion of the soul, the beginning of life, sense and reason, and the wonderful work of God, in keeping man from being stifled in the womb, and from many dangers after his birth.

*{Thou hast granted me}* Heb. *Thou hast wrought with me.* Made as it were a covenant with me of life and favor. For *Wrought,* see on Chap. 14. 9. on *Bring forth.*

*{life}* You have given me a soul, whence comes life. A figure of the effect, for the cause; as on the contrary, sometimes, the soul is put for the life; as it is interpreted, Psal. 7. 5. You have quickened me in the womb, and brought me alive out of it.

*{and favour}* Some understand it of a reasonable soul, which is a special favor bestowed upon man above the beasts, though they also have life. Others understand it of God's mercy, in preserving the infant alive in the womb, after that he has quickened it; for it might easily be strangled in the narrow prison of the womb. This was a great favor; for if he had died in the womb, he had been little the better for his life, Chap. 3. 16. Eccl. 6. 3. Psal. 22. 9. Others take it for a continual course of blessings and comforts bestowed upon him in his life for the good of soul and body. Or comfortable provisions to keep him alive. As it is taken, Gen. 21. 23. & 24. 49. Exod. 15. 13. Ruth 1. 8. Others take it for one thing intended in two several words. You have given me life, which is a great favor; as continuance of life is promised as a great blessing, in the fifth commandment. There is no comfort in anything without it, and men would give anything for the retaining of it, Chap. 2. 4. Eccl. 9. 4. The third is the most likely. The word חסד signifies. 1. Mercy, or favor, or kindness, Judg. 8. 35. 2. Reproach, Prov. 14. 34. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{thy visitation}* Or, *oversight.* Your providence and care of me. This visitation is not to condemnation, as Jer. 5. 9. Hos. 9. 7. nor for correction, as Psal. 89. 32. nor for deliverance out of trouble, as Exod. 4. 31. but for preservation from those evils, that might have taken away his life before now. So masters oversee their families, that no man may hurt their children or servants.

*{hath preserved}* You did not leave me to shift for myself, when you had made me, but kept me from what might endanger my life. Hereby God's watchfulness over him is intended, and using his almighty power, to keep him from deadly dangers, Psal. 121. all over. A similitude taken from shepherds, that watch over their flocks, Jer. 31. 10. For *Preserved,* see on Chap. 2. 6. on *Save.*

*{my spirit}* Not meant of soul or courage, as sometimes, but of life itself, which is gone when the spirit departs, Gen. 25. 8, 17. So that in this verse, he sets out God, first, as the giver of life; secondly, as the provider of things needful for continuance of life; thirdly, as the protector of men, against deadly dangers. For *Spirit,* see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Vain.*

**V. 13. ***{And these things hast thou hid in thine heart: I know that this is with thee}* Though you have thus accurately made me, and carefully preserved me, yet you have kept closely within you, a purpose to afflict me. How are these two sociable? So men purposing revenge, watch for a fit time; and make show of good will in the meanwhile. Though you did me much good, yet have you a secret purpose to plague me, as now you show. This coherence suits best with the following verses. This is a grievous complaint, and shows how far Job was transported in his passion, that compares God to an enemy, watching for a fit time, to take him unawares, to molest him.

*{And}* Or, *Yet.* So it is used, verse 7, 8.

*{these things}* Intentions of bringing evil upon me your creature.

*{hast thou hid}* Laid up closely, so that I could never perceive it, till these sorrows overtook me. Or, it may be read with an interrogation, *And hast thou hid these things in thine heart?* The answer follows, I know you have.

*{in thine heart}* Kept them close from my knowledge, as men do things they keep in mind, but utter not. For *Heart,* see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{I know that this is with thee}* I am sure that you have dealt thus closely with me. Or, I know it by experience, and by feeling these sorrows upon me. They make it plain to me. So the word is used for experimental knowledge, Hos. 9. 7. Others, understand it of God's eternal purpose to make Job in that accurate way, spoken of before; but that agrees not well with Job's scope, nor the words following.

**V. 14. ***{If I sin, then thou markest me, and wilt not acquit me from mine iniquity}* It appears by your harsh dealing with me, that you had a purpose to afflict me, for I cannot any sooner commit a sin, though but out of human infirmity, but you presently lay load upon me, and will not leave punishing me, till I be destroyed.

*{If}* Or, *When.* As Amos 7. 2. Job 7. 4. As oft as I sin, you note it, as it were in table-books, to punish me for it afterwards, Chap. 14. 16. & 31. 4.

*{I sin}* I err, or go never so little out of the way, that you have set me. The word signifies missing a mark, Judg. 20. 16. And so metaphorically sinning, which is missing the mark God sets before us.

*{then}* Heb. *and.* It is rendered *then,* Chap. 1. 25. & 2. 9.

*{thou markest me}* The word signifies, first, to preserve, by keeping out of evil and trouble, Psal. 141. 9. So it signifies, verse 12. of this chapter, Psal. 18. 23. Secondly, to keep in prison, Chap. 7. 12. 1 King. 20. 39. Thirdly, to watch exactly what we or others do, Psal. 39. 1. Jer. 20. 10. So here, You do watch over me, and observe all my thoughts, words and deeds, to get an advantage against me. For *Markest,* see on Chap. 2. 6. on *Save.*

*{and wilt not acquit me}* Nor pronounce me innocent. See Chap. 9. 23.

*{from my iniquity}* The word signifies crooked actions, such as run not level with the law of God, but turn aside from it, Chap. 7. 21. Others read it by way of question, thus, *If I have sinned, wilt thou keep me in prison, and not acquit me from mine iniquity?* As if Job had said, Suppose I had committed some great offense, which I do not confess myself to be guilty of, is it therefore fitting that you should keep me in prison still, as if I were a wicked man; or rather that you, who know that I hate wickedness, should pronounce me upright, and declare me to be so, by taking away these plagues from me? And so he confirms his former complaint, renewed in the former verse. *Iniquity,* is put for the punishment of iniquity, as Chap. 7. 21. Esay 53. 6. 1 Sam. 28. 10.

**V. 15. ***{If I be wicked, woe unto me; and if I be righteous, yet will I not lift up my head: I am full of confusion, therefore see thou mine affliction}* He shows by a pathetical dilemma, that he is every way miserable, and that whether he be a wicked man or no, he has no hope of escaping this misery that is upon him. Be I bad or good I must be punished.

*{If I be wicked}* If I be an ungodly man, as these my friends accuse me to be, Chap. 9. 22, 29.

*{woe unto me}* Then let me be punished more by God, and forced to cry out, *Wo is me,* Jer. 4. 31. Let me feel worse torments than I have. Or, I shall be quite undone. The word imports some great and extraordinary misery likely suddenly to fall upon him, Mic. 7. 1. 1 Cor. 9. 16. Some derive it from ילל, *to howl;* as men do in woe, Esay 13. 6. & 23. 1. Hos. 7. 14.

*{and if I be righteous}* If I be so, it is all one, I can look for no ease. By the word *righteous,* he means a godly man, as appears by the opposition to a wicked man, Chap. 9. 15, 20.

*{yet will I not}* Or, *yet can I not.* So the future is translated, Chap. 8. 11. See also on Chap. 9. 15. I am so broken with pain and grief, that I cannot look up with any comfort, though I were never so righteous; as verse 16.

*{lift up my head}* Men lift up their heads sometimes out of pride, Psal. 83. 2. And hang them down out of humility, as Isa. 58. 5. Sometimes the head is lifted up out of joy for some exaltation or deliverance had or promised; as Psal. 27. 6. Luke 21. 28. And hanged down for trouble threatened or inflicted, Lam. 2. 10. So Job here. He hangs down his head by reason of continual pains, and conceives no hope of freedom from them, that he might rejoice, but rather expects to sink under them. For *Lift up,* see on Chap. 11. 3. on *Suffer,* For *Head,* see on Chap. 19. 9. and 20. 16.

*{I am full of confusion}* The word *full,* or *satisfied,* argues abundance. I am as full of confusion, as men are of meat, that desire no more. So the word is used, Gen. 25. 8. Job 14. 1. The word *confusion,* in the original, comes from קלה, *to roast,* Jer. 29. 22. which is the greatest of torments. None like fire. He had shame, and burning heat within, which made him to blush outwardly. Confusion filled him because of his afflictions, and the hard censures of his friends. Confusion is more than shame. It distracts the mind, and makes a man not know which way to take. So it was now with Job. Or, it may be meant in respect of the shame that fell upon him in regard of his fall from so great riches, to so poor an estate, which made him to be contemned by those that honored him before. Some read it, *Be thou satisfied with my confusion.* As if he had spoken to God to entreat him not to lay more trouble on him, but to rest satisfied with the confusion his friends had brought upon him already. But the first reading is better; neither is the word *my,* in the original. Men are said to be *full of confusion,* that are overwhelmed with shame and contempt, Hab. 2. 16. Psal. 123. 4. For *Full,* see on Chap. 7. 4.

*{therefore}* So this particle is used, Chap. 11. 6.

*{see thou mine affliction}* If you will not credit me, look round about me, and you shall see nothing but affliction. You shall find my estate altogether as bad as I have reported it, Psal. 25. 18. & 119. 153. There David speaks it to move God to mercy; but Job here, to accuse God of cruelty in his distempered passion, as appears both by the words before and after. You know I am afflicted, but you take no compassion on me; but seem rather to hide your face, or look another way, Psal. 10. 1. By *affliction,* he means the weakness arising out of affliction, that lay as an heavy burden upon him: yet God seemed not to pity him, nor grace him with a good look. Some read it, *Being full of confusion, and seeing my affliction*. As if Job laid down the causes of his hanging down his head, to be confusion within, and affliction without. But the original words will not bear that translation. The former is more suitable to them. For *See,* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

**V. 16. ***{For it increaseth: thou huntest me as a fierce Lion; and again thou shewest thyself marvellous upon me}* Here Job shows the cause why he did complain so much of God's sharp dealing with him before, to wit, because of the increasing of his pains.

*{For}* So is this word used, to show a reason of a thing, Chap. 11. 4. Though commonly it couples things together, and signifies, *And.*

*{it}* My affliction.

*{increaseth}* Heb. *is lifted up;* or, *lifts up it self.* As an enemy it lifts up itself against me, or raises itself high, and domineers over me. The word when it is used of God or good men, it signifies excellency or glory, as Exod. 15. 1. when of ill men, it notes out pride, Job 40. 11, 12. when of senseless things, it signifies growing or increasing; as Chap. 8. 11. and so here.

*{thou huntest me}* You, O God, follow me, as close with plagues, as an hunter follows his prey with dogs. Sometimes he complains of his diseases, sometimes of God, as men in pain use to do, sometime blaming one cause, and sometime another.

*{as a fierce Lion}* One more cruel than ordinary, Chap. 4. 10. As a lion rampant hunts his prey, follows it close, lets it nowhere be quiet, till he has taken it, and torn it to pieces, so you give me no rest between my plagues, nor for ought I see, will give me any till I be utterly destroyed, Isa. 38. 13. Lam. 3. 10. Hos. 5. 14. Or, as men hunt a fierce lion, which leave not till they have killed him, lest he do more mischief; such an one you esteem me, and use me accordingly. See on Chap. 7. 12.

*{and again}* Heb. *and thou returnest.* When one affliction seems to grow toward an end, you presently clap another upon me. For *Returnest,* see on Chap. 15. 13. on *Turnest.*

*{thou shewest thyself marvellous}* You do not correct me in an ordinary way, but lay extraordinary plagues upon me, that men may wonder at me, Num. 16. 29, 30. above that which is common to men, 1 Cor. 10. 13. in extremity, above measure, 2 Cor. 1. 8. You do so press me with new and exquisite sorrows, as if you would show all your power in afflicting me, that the world might see what a wonderful workman you are in my troubles, Lev. 26. 21. Deut. 28. 59.

*{upon me}* Who am already half dead, like a weary beast lying down before a lion. Or, *against me.* As if I were your mortal enemy, so that you could never lay plagues enough upon me.

**V. 17. ***{Thou renewest thy witnesses against me, and increasest thine indignation upon me; changes and war are against me}* The greatness and frequent return of his troubles is further set out in this verse.

*{Thou renewest}* Still he attributes his troubles to God, though Satan had an hand in it. You give new strength to your witnesses against me, that they may not give over. Or rather you bring in fresh witnesses against me one after another. Which agrees best with the words following.

*{thy witnesses}* Some understand it of Bildad's following Eliphaz in pleading against Job. But it is rather to be understood of new and fresh plagues coming one after another upon him, which men would judge to be sent by God as witnesses of God's indignation against Job, to force him to confess some great wickedness committed against God; for great judgments use to attend upon great sins, and are esteemed by men to be open witnesses of secret wickedness. And are so often, though not always. So that Job complains, that God brought new witnesses against him like new moons, but always watery. As angry men, when one witness will not serve to condemn him, whom they accuse, bring more still, till it be done. So the Jews dealt with our Savior Christ, Matth. 26. 60. So witnesses are taken for plagues, Ruth 1. 21. Chap. 16. 8. And it agrees well with that which follows here, Mal. 3. 5.

*{against me}* To prove me a wicked man. Or, *Before me.* To my face, as witnesses to convince me openly, Gen. 31. 37.

*{and increasest thine indignation}* You send me daily more and greater plagues, as if your anger did not decrease, but increase against me daily.

*{upon me}* They press me down, as an heavy burden on a weary man. Or, *within me.* as Chap. 6. 4.

*{changes}* Divers afflictions, one coming after another, like waves in the sea.

*{and war}* Job had no open war now against him, but he means a multitude of troubles setting upon him like so many enemies. And as in besieging a town, fresh troops succeed those that are weary, till it be taken; so his troubles came daily upon him. See Chap. 6. 4. For *War,* see on Chap. 7. 1. on *Appointed time.*

*{against me}* 2 King. 13. 12.

**V. 18. ***{Wherefore then hast thou brought me forth out of the womb? Oh that I had given up the ghost, and no eye had seen me}* Seeing you take no pity of me in these sore afflictions, and will not hear my complaints to deliver me; why did you give me life at first, was it of purpose to make me miserable? This is worse, than if he had wished for present death; for it argues ingratitude for his life and the former comforts, disrespect of God's honor, which he had formerly promoted, and discontent with God his creator.

*{Wherefore then}* For what good end or purpose did you bring me into the world? It seems not to be for any good to me, I am now so miserable.

*{hast thou brought me forth out of the womb?}* Why did you give me strength to come alive into the world? Was I born only to suffer those evils, that I could not have suffered in the womb? See the same complaint, Chap. 3. 11. and one suitable to it, Num. 14. 2, 3. where the people of Israel make as light account of God's bringing them out of Egypt, as Job does here of his birth.

*{oh that I had given up the ghost}* The same wish, in effect, with the former; for if he had not died in the womb, but a little after, then some eye would have seen him, though but a while: Or, why died I not in the very birth, for then no eye could have seen me alive? Others read it, *Then had I given up the ghost,* &c. It had been much better with me then now it is. Giving up the ghost is often used for dying, Gen. 25. 8, 17. For when the soul goes out of the body, and breath fails, the body is dead.

*{and no eye had seen me}* No man had seen me alive; as follows verse 19. See Chap. 7. 8. & 13. 1. & 20. 9. & 28. 10. & 42. 5. Luke 2. 30. 1 Cor. 2. 9. Rev. 1. 7. He shows here what little account he made of all his former prosperity. He could have been content never to have enjoyed it, so he might have escaped his present troubles. Thus the servants of God, sometimes in heavy trials show themselves unthankful to God for all former favors. But this is their infirmity, Psal. 77. 10. For *Eye,* see on Chap. 7. 7. For *Seen,* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

**V. 19. ***{I should have been as though I had not been, I should have been carried from the womb to the grave}* Though I had been conceived in the womb; yet if I had not been born alive, I should have escaped all these miseries.

*{I should have been as though I had not been}* I should have been as free from these afflictions, as one that never had a being. My name would not have been heard among men, no more than theirs that never were. See Chap. 3. 16. Obad. verse 16.

*{I should have been carried from the womb to the grave}* I had not lived one moment to have felt the least sorrow: but should have gone immediately to the grave, the place of rest, Chap. 3. 18. He mentions the grave, as if that were likely to be the place of best rest to him; but afterwards he sets it out in a far other manner, verse 21, 22. So are good men transported with passion sometimes in their miseries, that they wish for things contrary to God's glory, and their own good. For *Womb,* see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Belly.*

**V. 20. ***{Are not my days few? cease then, and let me alone, that I may take comfort a little}* Job having formerly vented his passion, now begins to recollect himself; and considering his sore troubles, and likelihood of short continuance in this life, prays to God for some refreshing before he die; as Psal. 39. 13.

*{are not my days few?}* Certainly they are. So much is inferred in this question; as verse 3, 4, 5. He reckons by days, and not by years, to show, that his life was like to be very short, Chap. 7. 1, 6. & 8. 9. They are a small little thing, as the Hebrew word signifies, and so it is translated in the end of the verse. For *Days,* see on Chap. 14. 14. The word מעט signifies. 1. Small, Gen. 30. 15. 2. Few, Deut. 7. 7. So here.

*{cease then}* Or, *cease therefore:* as this particle is translated, verse 15. cease from afflicting me any more.

*{let me alone}* Leave afflicting me: Lay aside your rod for a while, Chap. 7. 16, 19. & 9. 34. Heb. *Cease and lay on.* That is, Cease from laying on.

*{that I may take comfort a little}* If you will not take my plagues quite away, yet give me a little breathing time, that I may taste once more of the comforts of life a little, before I die, which is likely to be shortly, Chap. 9. 27.

**V. 21. ***{Before I go whence I shall not return, even to the land of darkness, and of the shadow of death}* If comfort come not quickly, it will come too late. If I have it not before I die (which is like to be quickly) I shall never return into the world to have it.

*{Before I go}* Before I die, and so go out of this world; for death is a going into another world, Joh. 16. 7. and a going from all our friends and acquaintance here. Or, *Before I go into the land of darknesse.* And those words, *Whence I shall not return,* may be put in a parenthesis. For *Go,* see on Chap. 1. 4. and on Chap. 7. 9.

*{whence I shall not return}* In this, and the rest of this verse, and the following, is a description of the grave, or state of death. That it is a place, or state, from which there is no returning into this world, a place of darkness, &c. Not a description of hell, for Job never meant to come there, no more than Jacob, who uses the like speech, Gen. 37. 35. & 42. 38. He meant it of not returning into this world to enjoy those comforts he once had, and by death should lose. He desires therefore to have a little comfort in them, before he leaves them forever. And it is suitable to his friends' discourse, who say nothing to him of happiness, but use arguments of worldly prosperity to persuade him to repent. Job did firmly believe the resurrection of his body, Chap. 19. 29, 27. And therefore must needs speak here of not returning into this world. See the like, Chap. 16. 22. Psal. 39. 12. 2 Sam. 12. 23. For *Return,* see on Chap. 14. 13. on *Past.*

*{even to the land of darkness}* Having described death by a journey, he describes the grave, the house of death. Not a lightsome place, with a sun shining in it, but a dark land, Chap. 3. 5. & 17. 13. For *Land,* see on Chap. 9. 24. on *Earth.*

*{and of the shadow of death}* Most thick shadows, such as might make men to die for fear. Or such as the grave affords, if a man could see it, where there is a greater darkness than that of the night, even the darkness of death itself, Psal. 23. 4.

**V. 22. ***{A land of darkness, as darkness itself, and of the shadow of death, without any order; and where the light is as darkness}* He heaps up divers words of the same significations, as if no words could suffice to set out the horrid condition of death.

*{A land of darkness, as darkness itself}* He means the thickest darkness, and nothing but darkness, where no light at all is to be seen; as in the end of the verse.

*{and of the shadow of death}* See on verse 21.

*{without any order}* Without any distinction between day and night, light and darkness, between summer and winter, heat and cold, seedtime, and harvest, which continue here while the world lasts, Gen. 8. 22. No distinction between rich and poor, kings and beggars, great men and mean, which is a great part of the beauty of this world. No difference between prosperity and adversity, labor and rest: all is confusion. Men's bones are mingled in the grave, Chap. 3. 11, 15, 18, 19. Heb. *No orders.* No order at all in anything, but all confusion.

*{and where the light is as darkness}* The light is nothing but darkness. There is no light there at all, as no night in heaven, Rev. 21. 25. Heb. *The shining.* Not that there is anything in the grave, that shines; but that which is best there, is but darkness. No hope of any light, or comfort there. This is the highest expression of all. Darkness; as darkness itself, there being no worse thing to compare it to, and the best of it is but darkness; as 1 Cor. 1. 25. God's foolishness is wiser than men. God is not foolish, but in that action in which men think God shows least wisdom, he shows more than any man does in his wisest action. See the like phrase, Mat. 6. 23. or it may be read, *And when it shines here, it is darknesse there:* where, to conclude, there is palpable darkness, when the sun shines in all the world besides. Like Egypt's dismal darkness, when it was light in Goshen, Exod. 10. 23.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1. ***{Then answered Zophar, the Naamathite, and said}* Zophar insists in the footsteps of his two former companions, both for matter and manner of speech. But as it uses to come to pass in contention, that men's spirits grow hotter and hotter, so he sets upon Job more fiercely than the two former had done.

*{Then answered}* See Chap. 4. 1. & 6. 1. & 8. 1. And on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Zophar the Naamathite}* See on Chap. 2. 11.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 4. 1. And on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Should not the multitude of words be answered? and should a man full of talk be justified?}* Before Zophar begins, verse 4. to confute what Job had said, he objects three faults to him in the second and third verses. The first is multitude of words, in this verse. The second, Lying; and the third, Mocking, in the next verse.

*{should not the multitude of words be answered?}* An interrogation full of indignation, wherein Job is accused by Zophar of loquacity. Think you that none will answer you, because you are so full of words? Oftentimes men are silent, when they could answer; because they that begin to speak will never leave talking. And in such a case, it is fit men should be answered; yea, and interrupted also, if they will not give way to others to speak. See on Chap. 8. 2. For *Words,* see on Chap. 15. 3. on *Talke.*

*{and should a man full of talk}* Heb. *a man of lips.* A man that uses his lips immoderately in talking, as if he were made all of lips, and had no other members. So we call a great fighter, a man of his hands. Or lips may be taken for speech, or language; as Gen. 11. 1. and then he may either accuse Job of speaking too much, or too eloquently in his own cause; as if he would bear out a bad cause by many words, or cover it with elegant speeches. For *Man,* see on Chap. 1. 1. For *Lips,* see on Chap. 15. 6.

*{be justified}* He speaks not of the justification of a sinner before God but of a man in his cause. Is it fit that a prating fellow should carry away his cause? Arguments must be attended, and not empty words. Thus he seeks to stop Job's mouth with his own words uttered to the same purpose, if rightly translated, Chap. 6. 26. See the notes there. And shows withal, what just cause he conceives he had to answer Job, lest he should be puffed up after his long and eloquent oration, as thinking that nobody could answer it. The Heb. word צדק signifies. 1. To be just, or righteous, Gen. 38. 26. 2. To be justified, Psal. 51. 4. So here.

**V. 3. ***{Should thy lies make men hold their peace? and when thou mockest, shall no man make thee ashamed?}* He taxes Job of a second fault here, that fearing his multitude of words would not carry his cause, he had intermixed divers untruths to win the day by falsehood.

*{Should thy lies}* The word signifies such things, as men frame in their own heads; which being for the most part false, it is used for lies, Jer. 48. 30. It is used also for liars, Jer. 50. 36. He means those speeches that Job had used in his own defense, affirming himself to be innocent; as appears by his charge in the next verse. He alludes to his affirmation hereof, Chap. 6. 29, 30. and appeals to God as a witness, Chap. 10. 7. These Zophar accounts to be lies, and of so high a nature, that he could not hold his peace, but must needs reprove Job for them; as follows. The Heb. word בד signifies. 1. Alike, Exod. 30. 34. 2. Linen, 1 Sam. 2. 19. 3. A staff or bar, Exod. 27. 6. 4. A lie, Isa. 16. 6. 5. A liar, Jer. 50. 36. Here it is used in the fourth sense, for a lie.

*{make men hold their peace}* The word *Men,* in the original, is taken from death: Mortal creatures. So God puts us in mind of our mortality by our name מתים of מות. You do not speak divine revelations, neither can you boast that authority which you had in the time of your prosperity, that all men should hear with silence, and never reply, whether you speak true or false, Chap. 19. 21, 22. The word החריש signifies. 1. To practice secretly, 1 Sam. 23. 9. 2. To be silent, or hold one's peace, Psal. 28. 1. 3. To be deaf, Mic. 7. 16. 4. To make one hold his peace; as here.

*{and when thou mockest}* When you deride both God and us, seeking to persuade us to believe absurd things, Chap. 10. 3. & 34. 7. charging us with deceit, Chap. 6. 15.

*{shall no man make thee ashamed?}* Now he charges Job with a third fault; that seeing neither multitude of words, nor lying would uphold his cause; he sought to uphold it, by mocking those that should disprove him, and so damping their spirits, that they might not speak boldly to confute his lies, and cause him to blush for shame, and hang down his head, and cover his face, that he might be ashamed to speak any more so untruly, Num. 12. 14. 2 Sam. 19. 3.

**V. 4. ***{For thou hast said, My doctrine is pure, and I am clean in thine eyes.}* Zophar goes to work, as if he would deal more candidly with Job, than his fellows had done, and therefore names the question, which he would dispute, and cites Job's words for it, that he might not deny it. He does not out of malice feign these things, and father them falsely upon Job; for that would not become so wise and godly a man; but rather, he builds on Job's words misunderstood. The places he refers to, are Chap. 6. 10. & 9. 21. & 10. 7. Out of which he would, by a wrong consequence, gather this double charge of Job's maintaining the absolute truth and purity of his doctrine and life; whereas in the first place he did not maintain a freedom from all errors, but a willingness to speak the truth, according to his knowledge. In the second he maintains that truth, that God did afflict good men in this world, as heavily as bad men: Which is a truth, though Zophar thought otherwise. In the third, he openly calls God for a witness, That he was no wicked man, whatever his friends thought of him; but he nowhere affirms, that he had never sinned against God. Zophar therefore wrongs Job in putting other words instead of his, which not so much as by a just consequence, could be collected out of them. And so he condemns Job unjustly, and fights with his own shadow.

*{For}* The word commonly is translated, *And;* But it is often used to express a reason of a thing, or saying, and translated, as here, Prov. 8. 32. & 23. 3.

*{thou}* You, O Job, have spoken thus highly of yourself. If another had done it, we should have given more credit to it.

*{hast said}* Not only have spoken these words; but have seriously affirmed them for truth: as Psal. 116. 11. and that before God; as appears by the words, *In thine eyes.* For *Said,* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{my doctrine}* The word comes from לקח to receive, intimating the way of instruction, by receiving knowledge from parents, or teachers. And it is put sometimes for the things that are to be known, which indeed are worthy of all acceptation, as the apostle speaks, 1 Tim. 1. 15. So it is used, Deut. 32. 2. Prov. 4. 2. Sometimes for the knowledge of those things that are taught, as it is wrought in the mind of the learner; and it is used here in the first sense. Job had spoken the truth, and thought it worthy to be received, though his friends despised it. Some take it for manners, and good matters of life, received from others by counsel or example, and practiced by Job. But that seems rather to be intended in the following clause.

*{is pure}* It is unmixed, and clear like fair water: a man may see to the bottom of it, and see no mud there of errors.

*{and I am clean}* The word signifies pure from defilement, Psal. 24. 4. or a choice one, Neh. 5. 18. Cant. 6. 9. Job meant, that he was sound in doctrine, and upright in life; but Zophar takes him, as if he meant, that he was free from all error and sin.

*{in thine eyes}* In your sight. My friends take me for an hypocrite; but you know, that I am none. This was Job's meaning, to comfort himself with God's testimony against the false accusation of his friends. But they understood him, as if he did challenge God to declare any sin, whereof he was guilty. This phrase is often used in Scripture, to set out God's account of men and things, 1 Sam. 1. 15. 2 Sam. 11. 27. 1 King. 11. 33. For *Eyes,* see on Chap. 2. 12.

**V. 5. ***{But O that God would speak, and open his lips against thee}* Zophar conceiving that Job did maintain, that he was free both from error and sin, goes now about to confute that opinion. And because he knew not Job's heart, and though he would not be convinced by any man, though he spoke never so much reason, he wishes that God would, according to Job's desire, Chap. 9. 34, 35. interpose, and take his own cause into his own hand, and confute Job himself. And then he doubts not, but Job's mouth would be stopped.

*{But}* This particle is ordinarily translated, *And;* Yet sometimes, *But;* as here. See Job 6. 1. And in the original it is, *But surely;* as Chap. 1. 11. See there.

*{O that}* Heb. *who will give.* Zophar wishes earnestly, that God would take the cause into his own hand, Chap. 6. verse 8.

*{God}* The name comes from אל power. He is able so to speak, as to convince you; but we cannot do it.

*{would speak}* Would lay aside his authority, as you desire, and would reason the case with you, Psal. 50. 7.

*{and open his lips}* A similitude taken from men, who cannot speak without opening the lips. The same thing is intended that was meant before in the word *Speak:* But with a farther emphasis. For God could have spoken to Job by some secret sign, or by his spirit; so as no man might hear, but Job himself: but Zophar desires that God would so speak, as a man that opens his lips; that not only Job the party spoken to, might know his meaning; but himself, and his friends also that stood by; for he was very desirous to have Job's mouth stopped in his presence. The word פתח is used. 1. For opening the mouth, eyes, or ears, to speak, see, or hear, Chap. 3. 1. 2. For drawing out a sword out of the sheath, Psal. 37. 14. 3. For untying a knot, or sack, Gen. 44. 11. 4. For plowing or opening the earth, Isa. 45. 8. 5. For graving, 1 King. 7. 36. 6. For the appearing of grapes at first on the vine, Cant. 7. 12. 7. For opening a book to read, Neh. 8. 5. 8. For opening a gate, or door, Isa. 26. 2. Here it is used the first way. For *Lips,* see on Chap. 15. 6.

*{against thee}* Heb. *with thee.* That he would discourse and argue with you: yet it is sometimes translated *Against;* as Chap. 20. 27. And that was Zophar's desire, That God would plead against Job. And he made no doubt, but if God would but once open his mouth, and begin to speak, he would take his part, and his friends', against Job.

**V. 6. ***{And that he would shew thee the secrets of wisdom, that they are double to that which is: Know therefore, that God exacteth of thee less then thine iniquity deserveth.}* In this verse he expresses what is the end of his wish, that God would plead with Job; to wit, a persuasion that God would convince him of his great iniquity, and make him leave fretting against his proceedings with him.

*{And that}* Or, *For;* as including a reason of his former desire. So is it translated elsewhere. See verse 4.

*{he would shew thee}* Teach you so clearly to know this thing so exactly, as if it had stood before your eyes.

*{the secrets of wisdom}* The hidden ways of his providence, wherein he walks in his dealings with mortal men, which you think you are well acquainted withal; but would find then, that you are very ignorant of them. Secrets are called hidden things, because men cannot understand them, no more than they can see things hid from their eyes.

*{That they are double to that which is} Double,* in Scripture, is put for a great exceeding, Isa. 40. 2. & 61. 7. Jer. 17. 18. Zech. 9. 12. Rev. 18. 6. Some understand it of God's ways, that man cannot understand half the wisdom that is in God's ways, and so take it as a secret reproof to Job, that was as Zophar thought, too much conceited of his own wisdom. Others read it, *That they should be double to that which is.* And they understand it of Job's plagues; as if Zophar had said, That Job had no reason to be impatient, seeing God did not lay half the sorrows upon him that he did deserve; but his sins were far more than his sufferings, Ezra 9. 13. For *That which is,* see on Chap. 26. 3.

*{know therefore}* You seem to forget how gently God deals with you; take notice of it therefore upon my admonition. The word is translated *therefore,* Chap. 10. 15.

*{That God exacteth of thee less then thine iniquity deserveth}* He does not lay upon you all the punishment your sins have deserved; but lays far less load upon you. Others read it thus, *That God forgetteth thee in comparison of thine iniquity.* That is, that he punishes you so little, that he seems to have forgotten the greatness of your sin; and therefore you have no cause to think, that he deals hardly with you. The word נשה signifies. 1. To forget, Jer. 23. 39. 2. To borrow, or require a debt, Jer. 15. 10. So here.

**V. 7. ***{Canst thou by searching find out God? Canst thou find out the Almighty unto perfection?}* Here Zophar confirms what he had said before, That God's secret providence was far above the reach of man; and labors to convince Job of it: by his own infirmity, and inability to conceive the nature of heaven, hell, earth, and sea; all which are easier to be fully understood, than God's nature, or the course of his dealings with men. These things had Job set out excellently, Chap. 9. 4. &c. yet here Zophar vilifies him; Can you find out God? what can you know?

*{Canst thou?}* Assuredly you cannot: a vehement negation under an affirmative question; as Chap. 8. 11.

*{by searching}* By using the greatest skill you have, and your utmost endeavors to find out God's nature, or the course of his providence.

*{find out God?}* Come to the full knowledge of God, as follows in the end of the verse: or find out the reasons of his proceedings. You may think he deals unjustly with you, in laying such load upon you, when he knows causes to lay more on you, though he do not think fit to reveal them unto you. Others read it, *Canst thou find out the searching of God?* That is, his admirable wisdom, whereby he knows all things more excellently, than men do those things, which they have spent their brains most in searching out, Chap. 28. 27. 1 Cor. 2. 10. For God needs not search into anything, that knows all things exactly from eternity.

*{Canst thou find out?}* He repeats the same question, as knowing that Job could not answer affirmatively: and also explains it, by adding the degree of knowledge. For *Find,* see on Chap. 17. 10.

*{the Almighty}* Another title of God used frequently in Scripture, and fitly here to draw Job from resisting God, as Zophar thought he did.

*{unto perfection}* Till you know him, and his counsels perfectly. This is too great a task for you, or any creature to do. It goes beyond all created understanding. The word signifies the height, or greatest accomplishment of a thing. You may know something of God, and of his will. But nothing perfectly. Broughton expresses the former word by *depth;* and this by *scope.* Can you tell the utmost he aims at in afflicting you? No surely, Eccl. 3. 11. Rom. 11. 33.

**V. 8. ***{It is as high as heaven, what canst thou do? deeper then hell, what canst thou know?}* You are not able to comprehend the highest heavens, lower hell, nor the length of the earth, or breadth of the sea, much less the perfection of God's wisdom, which is higher than heaven, deeper than hell, longer than the earth, broader than the sea. If you could attain to them (which is impossible) yet must you needs fall short of the perfection of God's wisdom, which is far beyond all these. See the like dimensions, Eph. 3. 18.

*{It is}* God's wisdom, verse 6. or, *They are;* that is, the secrets of God's wisdom, verse 6. or, *He is;* that is, God: the Almighty, verse 7.

*{as high as heaven}* Heb. *The heights of heaven;* that is, as the highest heavens, which man's eyes cannot see, nor his understanding comprehend, Prov. 25. 3. It may be read, *It is higher then the highest heaven.* And then it answers well to that which follows, *Deeper then hell,* &c. So the defect in the former part of the verse is supplied out of the latter, Psal. 70. 1. *Make haste,* is added out of the end of the verse, to make the former part of it full and clear. For *Heaven,* see on Chap. 1. 16.

*{what canst thou do?}* Heb. *what canst thou work?* as Chap. 7. 20. Psal. 31. 3. all labor about impossible things is in vain. If you were called to give an account of the causes of things in heaven, as of the motion of the sun, moon, and stars, or rather of things in the highest and invisible heaven: What can you say to it? Nothing at all. Why do you then toil yourself in vain, to find out the hidden reasons of God's proceedings, which are far higher? It seems that he means the highest heavens, because he opposes it to hell.

*{Deeper then hell}* The word signifies the grave sometimes; but here it is put for hell, as appears by the opposition to heaven: as also, Mat. 11. 23. Hell therefore seems to be in the lowest parts of the earth. For *Hell,* see on Chap. 7. 9. on *Grave.*

*{what canst thou know?}* Or, *How canst thou know it?* So the word is translated, Chap. 9. 2. Deep things are hard to be found out, and sometimes impossible. Therefore God's judgments are compared to a great deep, Psal. 36. 6. How is it possible that you which cannot comprehend the depth of hell, should attain fully to the causes of God's proceedings with men, which are more abstruse?

**V. 9. ***{The measure thereof is longer then the earth, and broader then the sea}* He did not conceive that there were any dimensions or bounds of God's wisdom, but sets it out comparatively, making it to reach beyond the highest, deepest, longest, and broadest things that are, and so beyond all dimensions, to show that it is infinite, exceeding not only all things that are, but also all things that can be imagined.

*{the measure thereof}* The extent of it. Spoken after the manner of men, who know not the length of things, but by measuring them. But God's wisdom cannot be measured, Rom. 11. 33.

*{is longer then the earth}* As the heaven is set out, as the highest thing in Scripture, Prov. 25. 3. and hell for the lowest, Deut. 32. 22. so the earth for the longest. In this place. And the sea for the widest, Psal. 104. 25. See Psal. 139. 8. &c. Some kingdoms are exceeding long. How long then is the earth itself? For *Earth,* see on Chap. 9. 24.

*{and broader then the Sea}* The sea appearing more large to our eyes, than any part of the earth that we can see at once, because our sight is hindered by hills, trees, houses, that may be the reason why it is set out by breadth, and the earth by length; for narrow things seem long; and great things seem rather wide. God's wisdom goes beyond all these. For *Sea* see on Chap. 14. 11.

**V. 10. ***{If he cut off, and shut up, or gather together, then who can hinder him?}* You must know that God does not only govern all things wisely, and is able to give a good reason of all his proceedings, though you be not able to find it out, but also may justly use his power and will to dispose of things as he please, and no man has liberty to contradict him, though his ways seem strange to man's reason.

*{If he cut off}* The word signifies to change, or alter the condition of persons or things, and so by consequence to destroy.

*{and shut up}* Sometimes the word is taken in a good sense, for shutting one up to protect him and keep him from danger, that he may be safe, when others perish; as Deut. 32. 36. Sometimes in an ill sense, for shutting up in prison, 2 King. 17. 4. or delivering into the enemy's hand, so that there is no escaping, Deut. 32. 30. Psal. 31. 8. So here.

*{or gathering together}* Set men at liberty, so that they may dwell together freely, and thrive and increase, Zeph. 3. 18, 19. and then it is contrary to the former. Then the sense is, Whether God destroy or uphold, do hurt or good, no man can question him for it. If he should do and undo, confound all things, yet he were blameless. But it may rather be applied to afflictions. If God cut off men by the sword, or shut them up in prison, or bundle them up like sticks for the fire, or gather several afflictions into an army together against any man, he has no cause to complain. This suits best with Zophar's intention, to show that Job complained without a cause. Neither will any man demand a reason why God prospers him; but why he afflicts him.

*{then who can hinder him}* Heb. *turn him away.* To wit, from what he intends to do. See on Chap. 9. 12. For *Turn* see on Chapter 15. 13.

**V. 11. ***{For he knoweth vain men: he seeth wickedness also; will he not then consider it?}* Though he might use his absolute power to destroy men without a cause, yet he does not use to do so, for he can easily see vanity and wickedness in the hearts of them that appear saints to men, as you, O Job, have done. This you cannot deny. Tell me therefore why should not God punish iniquity, where he sees it?

*{For}* Though it may seem strange to men, yet God afflicts not without a reason.

*{he knoweth}* By a knowledge of intuition, not of approbation, as sometimes the word is used, Psal. 1. 6.

*{vain men}* Heb. *mortals of vanity.* By *vain men,* are meant rash and inconsiderate persons, or liars and unjust men, as the word sometimes imports, Exod. 20. 7. Psal. 24. 4. & 62. 9. Or ungodly men; as Psal. 26. 4. For *Vain* see on Chap. 18. 7. on *Strength.*

*{he seeth}* Knowledge and sight may differ in men, the one being an act of reason; the other of sense. But in God they are all one. Lest any man should think God does not know secret wickedness, he explains the former word *knowing,* by *seeing,* that is, he does as perfectly know those things which are hid from the world, as men do those things which they see with their eyes. We count sight the surest sense, and therefore prefer one eye-witness before ten ear-witnesses. For *See* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{wickedness}* He not only knows men, but all their sins also. Psal. 10. 11, 14. & 35. 22. The word sometimes signifies *grief;* for ungodly men, as they bring much grief to others by their unjust courses, so also much trouble and sorrow upon themselves, Psal. 10. 7.

*{will he not then consider it?}* View it diligently, so as to take full notice of it, Psal. 37. 10. so as to put a difference between it, and goodness, Lev. 10. 10. 1 King. 3. 21. Certainly he will take notice of it, take it to heart, and make men to see that he does so by exemplary punishments, Hos. 6. 10. Gen. 18. 20, 21. He closely taxes Job of hypocrisy, and wishes him not to trust to vain shows, for God would assuredly find him out.

**V. 12. ***{For vain man would be wise, though man be born like a wild asses colt}* Zophar goes on tacitly to accuse Job of foolishness and pride.

*{For}* Or, *Yet;* as Psal. 119. 109, 110. Though he be not able to reach to the depth of God's judgments, yet he would be thought to be a wise man, and will censure God's proceedings.

*{vain man}* Heb. *Hollow man.* That is, empty man; for hollow things have nothing but wind within. So the word is rendered, Exod. 27. 8. He means, that man is naturally empty of knowledge. For *Man* see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{would be wise}* Heb. *would be hearty.* The heart is the seat of wisdom. See on Chap. 9. 4. therefore *possessing an heart,* in the original, is translated, *getting understanding;* Prov. 15. 32. And fools are said to be heartless, Hos. 7. 11. The meaning is, though there be no true real wisdom in man by nature, yet he would be esteemed wise by others, and thinks so of himself, and therefore will be meddling with God's secrets, which he cannot understand; verse 6, 7, &c.

*{though man be born}* He shows whence this ignorance in man proceeds. Not from creation, for God made him wise, but from his birth. Ignorance of spiritual things is a part of original corruption. The Hebrew word אדם signifies. 1. A man in general, Psal. 49. 20. 2. It was the name of the first man, Gen. 4. 1. Here it is used in the first sense; For Adam was not born; nor made foolish.

*{like}* It is necessarily added, to set out the similitude, though it be not in the original, as appears by the distinction of the character. So as is added Chap. 7. 9. though not expressed, Chap. 7. 7. neither in the original, nor translation.

*{a wild asses colt}* To set out man's ignorance the more, he compares him to an ass, the silliest of all beasts; and therefore we call a fool, an ass. Nay to a wild ass, which is more foolish than a tame one; Nay to a *wild asses colt,* which is most foolish of all. Man is often compared to beasts in Scripture, Psal. 49. 20. & 73. 22. & 92. 6. Eccl. 3. 18. yet we are sent by Job himself to the beasts to learn, as if they were wiser than we, Chap. 12. 7.

**V. 13. ***{If thou prepare thine heart, and stretch out thine hands towards him}* Zophar goes on in the same method that his two fellows had done: Having laid load upon Job, and endeavored to show him his wickedness, now he persuades him to supplication and reformation, by many specious promises of restoration of peace and prosperity to him in abundance.

*{If thou}* The pronoun is not included in the verb, but plainly expressed in the Hebrew text. As if he had said, I speak to you, O Job, in particular. Though you have been a grievous sinner, yet if you pray to God aright, and reform your life according to his will, you shall be restored to a flourishing condition again before your death.

*{prepare thine heart}* By the *heart,* here, is meant not that fleshy part of the body, which lives first, and dies last, but the soul; as Psal. 41. 1. By preparation, putting the faculties in tune to seek and pray to God; as 2 Chr. 12. 14. & 19. 3. Psal. 57. 7. & 108. 1. He does not say, If you prepare *your lips,* but *your heart.* Noting, that his prayer must be sincere, and come from the heart, else God will not regard it. For *Heart* see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{and stretch out thine hands}* That is, If you pray to God for pardon of your sins, and stand no longer to justify yourself. It was the gesture of those that prayed in those times, not to hold their hands together, or lift them up a little way, but to stretch them out as far as they could toward heaven, as if they would pull down a blessing from thence, Exod. 17. 11, 12. 1 King. 8. 22. Psal. 141. 2. Thereby they showed, that their hearts were lifted up to God, and that they expected all help and succor from him, Lam. 3. 41. Stretching out of hands was also used as a gesture of sorrow, Jer. 4. 31. and as a sign of pity and bounty, Isa. 65. 2. But here, as a gesture of prayer, wherein we with sorrow for sin stretch out our hands to God, and he stretches out his to us in pity to pardon us, and bounty to relieve us. For *Hands* see on Chap. 36. 32. on *Clouds.*

*{towards him}* Towards God. Mentioned in verse 5, 6, 7. That is, towards heaven, which in Scripture is called God's throne, Isa. 66. 1. and whither we are directed to send up our prayers to him, Matth. 6. 9.

**V. 14. ***{If iniquity be in thine hand, put it far away, and let not wickedness dwell in thy tabernacles}* If you should prepare your heart aright, and pray earnestly to God at present for pardon of sins, yet that will do you no good, unless you reform your life and conversation.

*{If iniquity}* It may be meant of any kind of sin; but it is likely Zophar meant it here of cruelty and oppression of others; as if God by laying these sore afflictions upon him, did as it were from heaven testify against him, that he had gotten his former great estate by oppression, which is the thing that Job's other friends do cast in his dish, Chap. 4. 8, 9. & 11. 14. For *Iniquity* see on Chap. 18. 7. on *Strength.*

*{be in thy hand}* The seat of iniquity is in the heart, but it is often attributed to the hands, because they are the instruments of action in the course of our lives. See Psal. 7. 3. 1 Sam. 26. 18. 1 Chr. 12. 17. Psal. 26. 10. Chap. 16. 17. Iniquity, and especially violence, is like dirt or spots sticking on men's hands, which makes them unfit to be lifted up to God in prayer, Isa. 1. 15. Jam. 3. 7, 8. He intimates, that it is vain for Job to pray for mercy, till his hands be cleansed from spots of violence in God's sight. He may either mean the guilt of violence remaining upon Job unrepented of, or things gotten by violence kept still in his possession, and not yet restored to the right owner. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{put it far away}* Keep your hands from violence at a very great distance for time to come. Let it not come near you. Or, be far from keeping ill gotten goods; and if ever God make you able, make full restitution to those you have wronged.

*{and let not wickedness}* That is, injustice. Or, things unjustly gotten, Lev. 19. 15. Psal. 7. 3.

*{dwell}* Let it not abide. You cannot, it may be, keep it clean out, but hasten it away, by reformation or restitution, Prov. 3. 28. Psal. 5. 4.

*{in thy tabernacles}* In the men of your house. A good man must not only reform himself, but his household also. Or, let nothing gotten by fraud and violence abide in your dwelling, but show your repentance by sending it home to the right owners. See Chap. 5. 24. He may speak in the plural number, with allusion to Job's former estate. For rich men have divers tents or tabernacles, or at least divers rooms in their tabernacles. So God's house is called tabernacles, Psal. 84. 1.

**V. 15. ***{For then shalt thou lift up thy face without spot, yea thou shalt be stedfast, and shalt not fear.}* In the two former verses, Zophar had exhorted Job to submission, and reformation: now he gives him arguments of encouragement to persuade him so to do, from the full deliverance from present miseries, and full fruition of future prosperity.

*{For then}* When you have truly humbled yourself before God, and put away your iniquity.

*{shalt thou lift up thy face}* A phrase something like that before, of lifting up the head. See Chap. 10. 15. The lifting up of the face betokens. 1. Cheerfulness, when a man's conscience being pacified, and he assured of pardon of sin, may not only look man, but God in the face with comfort, Chap. 22. 26. 2. Strong confidence, whereby a man is confident he shall be upheld by God against all enemies spiritual and corporal, and need not fear hurt from God himself, 2 Sam. 22. 3. reverence to our betters, Deut. 28. 50. *not regarding the person of the old,* is in the original, *not lifting up the face to or of the old man.* Here it may be taken in the two former senses. You shall be full of comfort, but especially of confidence, as appears by the opposition in the end of the verse. A guilty conscience makes the countenance fall, Gen. 4. 6, 7. A clear conscience makes a man look up boldly. For *Lift up* see on Chap. 11. 3. on *Suffer.* For *Face* see on Chap. 14. 20. on *Countenance.*

*{without spot}* Or, *out of spot.* Being delivered from all the sores, and plagues and punishments of your sins, which have defiled your face, and made you hang down your head for shame and grief. Spots are literally bodily blemishes, Jer. 13. 23. metaphorically sins which defile the soul, Jude verse 12. But here it signifies a freedom from the least plagues or troubles, which are as it were spots in a prosperous estate. As appears by the verses following, which do not set out freedom from sin, but from misery.

*{yea thou shalt be stedfast}* You shall be so free from the least trouble, that you shall not only get, but also enjoy a constant prosperity. The word signifies *molten.* Things that are melted are afterwards very strong, as pillars, 1 King. 7. 15. 1 Sam. 2. 8. You had a good estate once, O Job, but now it is gone, chap. 29. 6. But if you truly turn to God, you shall have a better, and it shall be constant.

*{and shalt not fear}* You shall be so far from having any sensible loss of your prosperity, that you shall be freed from the fear of any evil or change, as long as you live. You shall not need to hang down your head, as now you do; by reason of these sorrows, nor to fear any more or greater troubles coming on you, which now you complain of so much, Chap. 10. 15, 16, 17. The Scripture uses to set out complete felicity, by expulsion of *fear.* For a rich man is not fully happy so long as he may fear poverty. But when either there are no more troubles, or none that can befall us; then our prosperity may be said to be complete. See Zeph. 3. 13. Isa. 17. 2. A bad conscience makes men afraid, but a clear conscience drives out fearfulness. He tells Job that he should not need after his true repentance to fear any more a revolution of any troubles.

**V. 16. ***{Because thou shalt forget thy misery, and remember it as waters that pass away}* He goes on in setting out the assurance of Job's constant prosperity after his repentance.

*{Because}* He gives a reason, why Job should not fear any future troubles. What need a man, or how can he fear that which is forgotten?

*{thou shalt forget}* Here also the pronoun is expressed for emphasis sake, as verse 13. Though you be thus miserable, and almost past hope of recovery, yet you shall be happy, if you repent. He does not mean, that it should not be possible for Job ever to think of his former troubles at all; for troubles past are thought of with delight, and make present prosperity the more pleasing; but he intimates; First, that there shall be no scar of his wound left, nor footstep of his troubles, to put him in mind of them to renew his grief, Gen. 41. 30. Secondly, that he shall have a long time of freedom from sorrows, which shall help much to make him no more affected with the remembrance of them, Jer. 50. 6. Isa. 54. 4. Thirdly, that he should have such abundance of blessings; that they should blot out the remembrance of his former sorrows, and give him no time to think of them, Isa. 54. 13, 17. As Joseph, in his height of prosperity in Egypt, forgot all his former miseries, in regard of any trouble of mind, arising from the remembrance of them, although he thought of them, sometimes with thankfulness to God for delivering him, and therefore called his eldest son's name Manasseh, Gen. 41. 51. Joh. 16. 21.

*{thy misery}* Or, *thy labour.* All your heavy pains you now endure and complain of so grievously.

*{and remember it as waters that pass away}* Or, *that are passed away.* If at any time they come into your mind, you shall think of them very slightly, as a man would think of waters that have overflown his ground, when afterwards he sees a great burden of grass upon it, and much cattle feeding there. This thought will not afflict such a man, no more shall the thought of your former sorrows ever afflict you more, Chap. 6. 15. 2 Sam. 14. 14. Or, You shall quite forget your misery, and remember it no more, than men do waters, that are quite gone away. For *Water* see on Chap. 14. 9. For *Pass* see on Chap. 15. 19.

**V. 17. ***{And thine age shall be clearer then the noon day; thou shalt shine forth, thou shalt be as the morning}* As he had encouraged Job before, to return to God by the certainty of future prosperity, so now by the extent and greatness of it.

*{And thine age}* So the word is translated, Psal. 39. 5. The remainder of your days shall be full of prosperity.

*{shall be clearer then}* Heb. *shall rise above.* That is, shall exceed the noonday in brightness. For *Rise* see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{the noon-day}* Which is most lightsome, the sun being then in his greatest height, and shining most bright. Your days shall be most comfortable and honorable. It is an usual thing in Scripture, to set out joy and glory, under the words of light and brightness, Psal. 37. 6. & 112. 4. Isa. 58. 10, 11. Esth. 8. 16. Prov. 4. 28. Job 5. 14. & 118. 5, 6. & 38. 15. Amos 8. 9. The word in Hebrew is of the dual number, because the noon, is between the forenoon, and afternoon.

*{thou shalt shine forth}* You shall appear honorable to others, and full of comfort. All men shall take notice of your prosperity. For *Shine,* see other significations of the same Hebrew word, Chapter 5. 7.

*{thou shalt be as the morning}* Isa. 58. 8. Some take it, as setting forth the sudden end of his troubles, and speedy turning his sorrow into joy, Psal. 30. 11. As the daylight comes suddenly after a dark night, whereas the noon-light comes by degrees, though, at length, it be clearer than the morning-light. Others conceive, that it sets out an increasing prosperity. Though it be great at first, yet it shall grow greater and greater still, as the morning-light does, whereas the noon-light is at the highest, and grows lesser and lesser till night. His meaning is, You shall not only have great joy, honor, but you shall have it suddenly, and it shall increase daily. As the sunrising in the morning is very welcome to men, after a dark night: So shall your prosperity be to you, after all your troubles.

**V. 18. ***{And thou shalt be secure, because there is hope; yea, thou shalt dig about thee, and thou shalt take thy rest in safety}* Job's future prosperity intimated in the former verses, is branched into divers particulars in this verse and the next; as security, safety, rest, fearlessness, and preeminence above others. And lastly, it is illustrated by the contrary condition of such as persist in sin, without repentance, in the last verse.

*{And thou shalt be secure}* It is not to be understood of carnal security, as Judg. 18. 10. Job 12. 6. but of an holy and confident resting upon God, as Psal. 21. 7. & 27. 3.

*{because there is hope}* He does not mean an hope of getting out of these miseries, for that is already supposed in the former verses. He speaks here of a further hope, which he should have, when his prosperity should be renewed. And that hope may be double; First, Of continuing that happy condition to which he should be restored. Secondly, Of daily increase of happiness. God would, by giving new favors, daily strengthen his hope of not returning into his sad condition, in which he now was, and of expectation of greater favors from him.

*{yea, thou shalt dig about thee}* Some interpret it of laying the foundation of an house, by an allusion to the Arabians, who using to change habitation, often dug holes, to fasten stakes to uphold their tents, where they found good pasture for their cattle. Others, of digging of wells, which was the first thing they used to do, where they meant to pitch their tents, that they might not want water for themselves, nor their cattle; as Gen. 26. 18, 21, 22. Others, of digging, or tilling the earth, and exercising himself in a lawful calling, with the hope of thriving. So the word is used, Luke 16. 3. Others, of entrenching himself, as cities, and armies use to make trenches round about, that they may not be suddenly surprised by the enemy. This sense agrees best with the words that follow, and with God's former dealing with Job. Chap. 1. 10. For *Dig* see on Chap. 39. 21. on *Paweth.*

*{and thou shalt take thy rest}* As you shall dwell safely, so you shall take your rest quietly in the night, not disquieted with cares, because God has given plenty. This blessing God gives to his beloved, Psal. 127. 2. For *Take rest* see on Chap. 14. 12. on *Lie down.*

*{in safety}* The word signifies both confidence within, and safety without, to show, that God's people are not confident in vain. It shall be to them according to their faith, Matth. 8. 13. & 15. 28. You shall sleep, as without care, so without fear, as being compassed about with a strong trench, to keep you from all your enemies, Lev. 26. 5. Psal. 3. 5. & 4. 8.

**V. 19. ***{Also thou shalt lie down, and none shall make thee afraid; yea, many shall make suit unto thee}* He shows, that God would not give him one blessing alone, but would clean forget all his sins, and add one mercy to another.

*{Also thou shalt lie down}* A metaphor taken from beasts, that couch and lie on the earth, when they go to sleep, Ezek. 19. 2. Psal. 23. 2.

*{and none shall make thee afraid}* You shall sleep as safely, as the sheep in a fold under a vigilant shepherd, where no wild beasts dare come near them. You shall have no enemy that dare interrupt your quiet estate, no not when you are asleep, Gen. 49. 9. Lev. 26. 6. Prov. 3. 24. In the former verse there was a promise of freedom from cares and inward fears, that might disturb his rest, here, from outward enemies, that might come upon him in the night, and fright him, and disturb his rest, which is not unusual in times of war.

*{yea many shall make suit unto thee}* Heb. *shall entreat thy face.* They shall not despise you, as they do now, because of your troubles, but you shall be so rich, and in so great authority, and so full of power, that not some few, but multitudes shall seek unto you for counsel, help, right, or preferment. So Zophar himself was fain to do, Chap. 42. 8. For the phrase, see Exod. 32. 11. 1 Sam. 13. 12. 1 King. 13. 6. Psal. 27. 8. & 45. 12. & 119. 58. Prov. 19. 6. Zech. 7. 2. The Hebrew word רב signifies. 1. Much, or many. Psal. 34. 19. 2. Great. Numb. 11. 33. Here it is used in the first sense. But it may be translated *Great ones.* And then the meaning is, Not only mean men, but great ones also shall need your help, and apply themselves unto you for favor, which is a very great honor. See the like Psal. 45. 12. 72. 10, 11. The word חלה signifies. 1. To make sick. Deut. 29. 22. 2. To make suit to one, or entreat him. 2 Chron. 33. 12. So here. For *Face* see upon Chap. 14. 20. upon *Countenance.*

**V. 20. ***{But the eyes of the wicked shall fail, and they shall not escape, and their hope shall be as the giving up of the Ghost.}* Having before set out the happy condition of such as return to God, to encourage Job to do so, now he declares the misery of them that continue in impenitency, to discourage Job from continuing in that evil way, where Zophar thought he walked. The misery of such he sets out in three particulars: 1. The tedious expectation of good. 2. No escaping out of evil. 3. Hope ending in despair.

*{But the eyes}* The eye is a principal member, and great judgments in Scripture are set out by some plague upon the eyes which are very tender and useful, Chap. 3. 10. Psal. 38. 10. Prov. 30. 17. For a blind man is in a sad condition, and the more sad, if he once enjoyed the benefit of sight. For *Eyes* see on Chap. 2. 12.

*{of the wicked}* Chap. 3. 17. & 8. 22. & 10. 3.

*{shall fail}* Shall wear away like a cloud that vanishes, Job 7. 9. They shall be so weary with expecting good, which they shall never enjoy, that they shall be able to look up no longer, Lam. 4. 17. Chap. 31. 16. For *Fail* see on Chap. 16. 11. on *Spend.*

*{and they shall not escape}* Heb. *flight shall perish from them,* Amos 2. 14. They shall be beset with so many miseries, as so many enemies, that they shall have never an hole left to flee out at. As they shall not obtain the good they expect, so they shall never be able to escape the miseries they feel or fear. Or, *Refuge shall perish from them;* as Psal. 142. 4. There shall be no shelter or place of refuge for them to fly to, to escape those storms. They shall in vain seek for help of God or man; as Saul did, when he had forsaken God, 1 Sam. 28. 6, 15.

*{their hope shall be as the giving up of the Ghost}* There shall be no more hope of prosperity for them, than of a man's life, that is giving up the ghost. Their hope is dying and decaying, Chap. 8. 13, 14. & 18. 14. Or, *Their hope shall be the giving up of the ghost;* They shall see themselves so quite out of hope of any good, that they shall wish themselves dead, Chap. 3. 21, 22. Jer. 15. 9. It is translated, *losing of life,* Chap. 3. 39. This latter interpretation is best, there being no note of similitude in the original. For *Hope* see on Chap. 8. 13. For *Ghost* see on Chap. 14. 22. on *Soul.*

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1. ***{And Job}* In the three following chapters Job answers Zophar, and to take away that imputation of ignorance which his friends laid upon him, he shows God's proceeding in governing the world, more excellently than they had declared it. For Job's name and person, see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{answered and said}* See on Chap. 3. 2. & 4. 1. And on Chapter 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{No doubt, but ye are the people, and wisdom shall die with you}* Here Job sets out that great opinion his friends had of their own wisdom, and scoffs at it in an ironical way. Though you think you only know the course of God's providence, and censure me as boldly as if you were of God's privy council; yet I shall show you your error and folly.

*{No doubt but}* Or, *Of a truth,* as it is translated, Chap. 9. 2. It is of the same root with *Amen,* and imports certainty. I know you think so highly of yourselves, and would have others to think so of you also.

*{ye are the people}* He speaks to all three; for they all agreed in condemning him. You conceive that you are a wise people, and more excellent than others for knowledge, Deut. 4. 6. And others are but beasts in comparison of you. You think you are a select people. So, *a man,* is put for *a good man,* Jer. 5. 1. Or, you imagine, that you three are as a whole nation, and all the wit in the nation, is in your heads.

*{and wisdom shall die with you}* You think that when you are dead, all the wisdom in the world dies with you, none being left, that are able to instruct others. Thus Job ironically scoffs at his friends' pride, who had laid him so low, and set up themselves so high; and pays them with their own coin, Chap. 11. 12.

**V. 3. ***{But I have understanding as well as you; I am not inferior to you: yea who knoweth not such things as these?}* Though you think so highly of yourselves, and contemn others, yet I may come into the balance with you for wisdom, and claim as great a share in it as you. Nay the vulgar sort of people know these things as well as you.

*{But}* Heb. *Also.* You need not appropriate all wisdom to yourselves: you may take me also into your number. For *Also* see on Chap. 16. 4.

*{I have understanding}* Heb. *I have an heart,* The heart is put for the understanding sometimes, because it is by many conceived to be the seat of understanding. But because most think the brain to be the seat of knowledge, the heart may be taken here metaphorically, for the soul, as Psal. 45. 1. & 49. 3. Prov. 4. 23. & 25. 16. And then by another figure of the whole for the part, the soul for the understanding faculty, which is a prime one, and the light of the whole soul. See on Chap. 9. 4. & 11. 12. See the word thus used, Chap. 34. 34. Jer. 5. 21. It is *heart* in the original in both places, but translated *understanding,* as here. Yet the heart is put sometimes for courage, as 2 Sam. 17. 10. Jer. 48. 41. But that suits not well with the scope of this place. For *Heart* see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{as well as you}* I understand the course of God's providence, as much as you do. For this is the knowledge here spoken of.

*{I am not inferior to you}* Heb. *I fall not lower then you.* A metaphor from men conquered, who fall down before the conquerors. So the word is used, Neh. 6. 16. The enemies seeing how God dealt with his people, they fell in their own eyes. They thought more meanly of themselves than they did before. So Esth. 6. 13. I am not fallen from the knowledge of these things no more than you. See the same phrase, Chap. 13. 2. But there is yet more in this phrase. For Job did not mean, that he thought himself equal to them in the knowledge of God's providence, whose errors therein he goes about to confute, but that he was beyond them in this point, and understood it better than they did, though in his modesty he would not say so much of himself, as he might have done. A figure called Meiosis, and often used by godly men in Scripture; as 1 Cor. 7. 40. 2 Cor. 7. 10. & 11. 5, 21, 23. & 12. 11. with 1 Cor. 15. 10.

*{yea, who knoweth not such things as these?}* Heb. *and with whom are not such as these?* You think you have a great treasure of knowledge more than I, and yet, not I only, but many meaner than I, yea, every common man understands God's way, in his dealing with good and bad men, as well as (if not better than) yourselves.

**V. 4. ***{I am as one mocked of his neighbour: who calleth upon God, and he answereth him: the just upright man is laughed to scorn}* As before he had objected to his friends too slight an opinion of him. For men think very meanly of them, whom they deride.

*{I am}* You need go no further for an example to confute your opinion, that God always upholds and countenances godly men: you may see the contrary in me, whom God, not only afflicts with heavy plagues, but also gives over to be derided by you.

*{as one mocked}* Heb. *a laughter,* or, *derision.* Mocked in the highest degree, as one fit for nothing, but to be made a laughingstock. There is no *as* in the original, and no question but Job meant it of himself, Chap. 16. 10. & 17. 2. & 21. 3. & 30. 1. Psal. 35. 16. & 44. 13. For *Laughter* see on Chap. 29. 24.

*{of his neighbour}* Before he spoke in the first person; *I am:* Now in the third, *of his neighbour.* As not willing to fall foul upon his friends directly; but giving them an hint, under another person, how unkindly they dealt with him. The word signifies, as a neighbor dwelling near one, so a friend or companion brought up together with one at the same table. For it comes from a word that signifies to feed, as cattle feed together, Psal. 35. 14. & 41. 9. Prov. 27. 10. This aggravates Job's misery, for mocks and taunts from friends, from whom we expect countenance and comfort, are most bitter, and hardest to be digested.

*{who calleth upon God}* Some understand it of the party mocked; or Job himself, who being wholly slighted by his friends, has no refuge to fly to, but God, to right him; and persuades himself that God will do it. But this agrees not with Chap. 9. 16. It is rather to be understood of the mocker, who was one that made a profession of the true religion, which is set out by that phrase of calling upon God, Psal. 79. 6. Jer. 10. 25. And it was a great addition to Job's misery, to be mocked not by Gentiles; but by such as professed the same true religion with him. Or it may be meant, that the mocker was so bold, that he durst pray to God to confirm his mocks, and to prosper him in his wicked course. For *Call* see on Chapter 13. 22.

*{and he answereth him}* God answers him really, by making the mocker to prosper in his evil way, and so encouraging him to deride others. His meaning is, that he had heaven and earth against him, and his deriding friends were heard of God, when he could not be heard. For *Answer* see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{the just upright man is laughed to scorn}* Job speaks modestly of himself in the third person, as if he spoke of another. So does Paul, 2 Cor. 12. 2, 3, 5. A man that is truly just, and not seeming so only, is subject to contempt and scorn.

**V. 5. ***{He that is ready to slip with his feet, is as a lamp despised in the thought of him that is at ease}* Here Job couches a secret reason, why he was despised and mocked by his friends; to wit, because he was brought to a low condition, which uses to be accompanied with contempt; and they were in a flourishing estate, which ordinarily carries pride along with it.

*{he that is ready}* The word is fixed, or prepared. He that cannot choose but fall, and is near to destruction, Psal. 108. 1.

*{to slip with his feet}* To catch a fall, or to *halt;* as Psal. 38. 17. Jer. 20. 10. He that is brought so low by afflictions, that he is ready to perish, Deut. 32. 35. Psal. 94. 18. Jer. 13. 16. Psal. 73. 18. So on the contrary, the firmness of an estate, is set out by the unmovableness of the foot, Psal. 121. 3. There is a falling into sin, set out by the slipping of the feet, Isa. 73. 2. But that agrees not with the coherence of this place.
