*{is as a lamp despised}* He that is going down the wind in the world, is like a lamp that once made a fair show, and was much esteemed, while men might guide themselves by the light of it; but when it is well nigh out, it begins to stink, is slighted and cast away.

*{in the thought}* Heb. *to the thought.* It is so represented to their mind by their corrupt judgment. It comes from a word that signifies clearness, or brightness, in the original; because as bright beams are shot forth by the sun, so self-pleasing thoughts come from the mind. The word is used, Psal. 146. 4. Dan. 6. 3. Jon. 1. 6.

*{of him that is at ease}* That is rich, and has the world at will, and therefore is not sensible of the miseries of other men. The word does not signify all sitting still, or being quiet, but such ease as rich men take that need not labor, but can live upon their means without it: as if he had said, Such an one as I, full of affliction, and near to destruction, am now despised like a lamp going out, and that by you my friends, who have the world at will, and live in ease, while I am in want, and full of pain. I had a shining time of prosperity, but now it is gone, and I am slighted by you.

**V. 6. ***{The tabernacles of robbers prosper, and they that provoke God are secure, into whose hand God bringeth abundantly.}* As it often goes ill with the godly; as verse 5. contrary to what you, O Zophar, have affirmed, Chap. 11. 17. so it often goes well with the ungodly, contrary to your assertion, Chap. 11. 20. This Job proves by observation, or experience, verse 5, 6. by testimony of the creatures, verse 7, 8, 9, 10. Of the senses, and ancient men, verse 11, 12.

*{the Tabernacles}* See on Chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14. By *Tabernacles,* is meant all they have, in, or about their dwellings, as their children, cattle, goods, and all their affairs.

*{of robbers}* It comes from a word that signifies to lay waste, as robbers do where they come, and as the Chaldeans and Sabeans had done to Job, Chap. 1. To which he may here allude. He means, such as deal most unjustly with men; yet they thrive wonderfully in the world.

*{prosper}* Or, *Are quiet.* They thrive by their stolen goods, and nobody molests them, Chap. 3. 26. Psal. 30. 6. For the prosperity of the wicked men, see Chap. 21. 7. Psal. 37. 1, 35. & 73. 12. & 92. 7. Jer. 12. 1. Hab. 1. 3, 4. Mal. 3. 15.

*{and they that provoke God}* The word הרגיז in the original, signifies, to move bodily, or shake: as Job 9. 6. Isa. 23. 11. And secondarily, to move to passion, or distemper, or anger, which unsettles the mind of man, 2 King. 19. 28. it is called rage. God is not said to be provoked by infirmities, or ordinary sins; but by heinous sins, or sins done with an high hand, and with aggravating circumstances, Psal. 106. 7. Not only such as offer violence to men (as in the beginning of the verse) but also such as sin immediately against God, and that with an high hand, prosper.

*{are secure}* Heb. *Confidences are to them.* They are not only quiet when others are in trouble; but also most confident of the continuance of their prosperity. The plural number notes out abundance of confidence; as of blessedness, Psal. 1. 1. Or *the blessedness of the man*, &c. And, *Wisdomes,* Prov. 1. 20. in the original, notes out abundance of wisdom. Or, It may be meant of strong places, or store of wealth, whereon a man builds confidence.

*{into whose hand}* The number is changed to the singular, to show, that God deals so not with some few, but with many particular wicked persons; as by hand, is meant, power, or possession; as Chap. 1. 12. & 2. 6. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{God bringeth}* Robbers get their goods unjustly; yet God is said to bring them into their power, because without his leave they could not meddle with them. See on Chap. 1. 21. Others read it, *To whom God hath brought by his hand,* Others, *Because God hath brought,* &c. The first reading is the best.

*{abundantly}* This word is added by the interpreters, in a distinct letter, to show the right meaning of the text; that God does not give sparingly to them, as to some others; but much more than to many good men.

**V. 7. ***{But ask now the beasts, and they shall teach thee, and the fowls of the ayre, and they shall tell thee.}* For the greater conviction of his friends, he brings his proofs from the lowest creatures. You need not think you have taught me such rare things, or send me to ancestors to learn (Chap. 8. 8, 9, 10.) that which brute beasts can teach. I need not prove by reason, or Scripture, what I say: I shall bring the beasts for witnesses against you.

*{But}* Heb. *And surely:* See on Chap. 1. 11. & 2. 5. There is no question, but the beasts may teach you so much.

*{ask}* Enquire how things are carried among the brute beasts. For *Ask* see on Chap. 8. 8. on *Enquire.*

*{now}* Or, *I pray thee:* Do not think much to be informed by them. For *Now* see on Chap. 4. 7. on *I pray thee.*

*{the beasts}* It may signify any kind of beasts, wild or tame: but it is commonly put for wild beasts, and the sense is the more full; There is no beast so wild in the wilderness, that never came near man to learn anything of him, but can inform you in this point. Because men use not reason well, God to shame them does often in Scripture send them to the beasts to learn, Prov. 6. 6. Isa. 1. 3.

*{and they}* Heb. *and it.* That is, every one of them. None so vile, or brutish, but can inform you. The like phrase of joining a noun plural, to a verb singular, is often found in the original, in the same sense; as Eccl. 10. 1. Gen. 49. verse 22.

*{shall teach thee}* If they could speak they would inform you in the truth of what is in controversy between us. Or, You may, by enquiry into their carriage, learn the truth herein, for there is matter enough from thence to be had to show you. So the heavens teach, Psal. 19. 1. It is true of God's providence in general. But here it is to be applied to the business in hand. You shall see the great beasts devour the little ones, the hurtful fowls destroy the harmless, the great fishes eat the smaller: a lively type of God's providence among men, suffering the bad to oppress the good, Hab. 1. 13, 14. For the word *teach,* see on Chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10.

*{and the fowles}* The word includes all flying birds great and small. Any of them can acquaint you with this.

*{of the ayre}* Heb. *of the heaven.* Not of the third heaven, wherein are the angels; nor of the second, wherein are the stars; but of the first. And therein not of the upper or hot region of the air, wherein some place the element of fire; nor of the middle region, wherein are the clouds; but of the lower region of the air, wherein the birds use to fly, Chap. 28. 21. Psal. 8. 8. For *Heaven* see on Chap. 1. 16.

*{and they shall teach thee}* They shall set it out plain before you, so that you may, as it were, see it with your eyes. So the Hebrew word signifies. It comes of נגד. Before, or in sight. They will make you to understand it clearly and fully.

**V. 8. ***{Or speak to the earth, and it shall teach thee; and the fishes of the sea shall declare unto thee.}* He proceeds to bring other witnesses of the truth of what he said; and that from the earth and sea.

*{Or speak to the earth}* Others read it; *Or ask the sprig of the earth.* Adding the word *ask,* out of the former verse. The words may bear it, and it is a distinct testimony from the rest, to demonstrate the point in question. As the great trees overthrow the smaller, and drop upon them, and keep the sunshine from them, and by reason of the greatness of the roots, afford the lesser ones no room to root in the earth, and suck away the moisture of the earth from them, and by all these means kill them, and that by God's disposing; so God permits the wicked to oppress good men in the earth. From the Hebrew word ארץ comes our English word *Earth.* It is used sometimes for the whole body of the earth, as Gen. 1. 1. Sometimes for a land, as 2 Sam. 3. 12. Sometimes for the inhabitants of the earth, Psal. 67. 2. Or of some particular land, as Gen. 41. 57. Sometimes for fields, or arable grounds, as Exod. 23. 10. Here in the first sense. For *Speak,* or *sprig,* or *bush,* see on Chapter 9. 27. For *Earth,* on Chap. 9. 24.

*{and it shall teach thee}* See on verse 7.

*{and the fishes of the sea}* These also, although more dumb than beasts, making not so much as a sound, yet may acquaint you, that as great fishes devour small, so tyrants oppress good people. For *Sea,* see on Chap. 14. 11.

*{shall declare unto thee}* The Hebrew word signifies to reckon up, or declare particularly: as 2 Sam. 24. 20. Psal. 26. 7. They are able to produce many such particular examples of violence in the seas.

**V. 9. ***{Who knoweth not in all these, that the hand of the LORD hath wrought this?}* Now he comes to a conclusion out of the former premises, that it appears by all these testimonies, that God brings forth all those troubles of good men in the world, as well as those among the beasts.

*{who knoweth not}* Who is so silly, as not to understand this course of God's providence, which is held out so plainly in every mean creature? Because Zophar, Chap. 11. 8. had with high words set out God's providence by unsearchable things, and Job had said, verse 3. of this chapter, that in this point controverted between them it was obvious to all, now he shows it by earthly creatures near at hand, and commonly seen by vulgar eyes. You need not seek it so far, that may be informed by those violences, which God permits in other creatures, and see how he deals with men. This he does to humble his friends, who were proud of their wisdom, as if they could bring forth such things as none else could.

*{in all these}* That God shows the footsteps of his providence in all these creatures at sea and land. Or, *by all these.* They are instruments to make it known. So ב is used twice in one verse, Psal. 33. 6.

*{that the hand of the LORD}* His divine power, by which he works, as men do, with their hands, Chap. 10. 8. For *Hand* see on Chapter 15. 23.

*{hath wrought this}* Ravening fowls, and beasts, and fishes, could not thus prey upon others; nor bad men on good, unless God had an hand in it. For the phrase, see 2 Sam. 14. 19. For *wrought* see on Chap. 14. 9. on *Bring forth.*

**V. 10. ***{In whose hand is the soul of every living thing, and the breath of all mankind}* It must needs be, that God has an overruling hand in all these confusions in the world, for the life of all creatures is at his disposing. He can take it away when he please, and so cause the stronger creatures to cease from oppressing the weaker, and bad men from oppressing good.

*{In whose hand}* In whose power or disposing. See on Chap. 1. 12. & 2. 6. Gen. 16. 6. Dan. 5. 23.

*{is the soul}* Or, *life.* The word נפש is put sometime for the soul itself; as Ezek. 18. 4. Sometime for the whole man; as Gen. 46. 26. Sometimes for life, the effect of the soul, for, it goes away from the body, when the soul departs, Gen. 9. 4, 5. Sometimes for a dead body, out of which the life is gone, Lev. 22. 4. Here it is taken for the life of a beast flowing from a sensible soul, Lev. 17. 10, 11. The life of a beast is in his blood. If that be let out, the beast dies. For *Soul,* see more on Chap. 14. 22.

*{of every living thing}* Of every unreasonable and sensible creature. For it is opposed to man's spirit in the end of the verse, and the Jews do not attribute souls to trees, Psal. 145. 16. It is sometime restrained to man, as Gen. 3. 20. But it cannot be so here, for the reason before given.

*{and the breath}* Sometimes it is put for the reasonable and immortal soul in man, as Eccl. 12. 7. Sometimes for the breath, as Psal. 146. 4. His breath being gone, life is gone withal. So it is taken here. For *Breath,* see on Chap. 15. 2. on *Vain.*

*{of all mankind}* Heb. *of all flesh of man.* That is, of every man. Flesh is taken sometimes for man, as Isa. 40. 6. *All flesh is grasse,* that is, all men are mortal. Sometimes for the substance of the body, as Gen. 17. 13. Lev. 19. 28. Heb. 12. 9. Sometimes for the original corruption of nature remaining in men, Gal. 5. 17. Here it is taken for the body of man. Though the word איש, be used sometimes for an eminent man, and sometimes for a male, as we have showed on Chap. 1. 1. yet here it comprehends both men and women. So also Exod. 10. 13. & 21. 16. Chap. 16. 16. & 34. 11. As God can take away the life of every beast, so the life of every man, and so end all oppression: and therefore he must needs have a hand in the disorders by his providence. For *Flesh,* see on Chap. 14. 22. For *Man,* see on Chap. 1. 1.

**V. 11. ***{Doth not the ear try words, and the mouth taste his meat?}* The intent of Job in these words is, that the truth of the thing in controversy between him and his friends might easily be known, if men would but examine the things that were spoken on both sides by himself, and by his friends. For there is as sure a way to judge of truth and falsehood by the ear, as there is of judging of pleasant and bitter things by taste. See the like, Chap. 6. 30. & 34. 3.

*{Doth not}* Spoken by way of admiration, and interrogation, as Chap. 6. 5. & 8. 10, 11. Certainly the ear can try words. A strong asseveration.

*{the ear}* The hearing faculty, yet not separated from the judging faculty of the soul; but as an instrument of it. Hence comes a word in the original, that signifies a pair of balances. For they are like the head with one ear hanging on the one side, and another on the other. And the understanding judges of the truth of words by the two ears, as the beam determines the just weight of things by the two scales. For *Ears,* see on Chap. 15. 21.

*{Try}* Thoroughly examine whether they be true or false, well proved or not; consequent out of the grounds they are built upon or not. Thus Job wishes his grief to be weighed, or tried, whether he had not just cause to complain heavily, Chap. 6. 2.

*{words}* That is, speeches, or sentences, and that so exactly, that it calls every word of the sentence to account. Words are the proper object of the ear, as colors of the eye.

*{and}* This word is often translated, *as.* See Chap. 5. 7. & 16. 21. and so it may here. And the meaning is, A man may give as true judgment of words by his ear, whether they be true or false, as of meats by his taste, whether they be sweet or sour.

*{the mouth}* Heb. *the palate.* For it is thought to be the proper instrument of tasting, rather than the whole mouth.

*{tastes his meat}* Heb. *tastes meat for it self.* That is, as a man by his palate tastes of meat offered to him, and if it be good receives it: if not, refuses it; so must I with your words, and you with mine, try them by the ear, and if they be found true, receive them; if not, reject them. And by this careful examination of reasons on both sides, we shall come to a true resolution of what is in controversy between us. For *Taste,* see on Chap. 6. 6.

**V. 12. ***{With the ancient is wisdom; and in length of days, understanding.}* If the soul can by the ear judge of the truth of words, then doubtless they may best know the truth of things, that have lived long, and had much time to weigh the things they have heard.

*{With the ancient} In the ancient.* The Hebrews have three words to set out an old man; 1. זקן, a man of sixty years old, and upward. 2. שב, a man of seventy and upward. 3. ישיש, a man of fourscore years and upward. This is the word here used. It is derived from יש, *To be.* And Job hereby intimates, that they which live longest, and have the longest being here, have a fair opportunity to know truth best.

*{is wisdom}* The knowledge of divine truths gathered out of long observation, and experience.

*{and in length of days}* That is, in men that have lived long, and seen many days. For *Days,* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{understanding}* As they have gotten wisdom by experience to know more than others, so likewise understanding to draw other things out of those they have observed, and so may well know much more than the younger men. Job herein grants what Bildad had affirmed, Chap. 8. 8. &c. yet with a secret retortion upon his friends, some of whom might be ancienter than he, Chap. 15. 10. yet understood not what he spoke concerning God's providence. And therefore some read it *by way of interrogation, Is wisdom with the ancient, and understanding in the length of dayes?* It should be indeed, but it appears not in you. You have lost much time, and want much understanding of divine truths.

**V. 13. ***{With him is wisdom and strength, he hath counsel and understanding.}* It may be old men may want understanding, but God does not. And whereas old men decay in strength, if they do increase in wisdom, God is infinite in both.

*{With him}* Not with the old man spoken of last, but with God, of whose providence all this dispute is, and who is mentioned often before in this chapter, verse 4, 6, 9. so that he needed not be named again, but his friends might easily perceive whom he meant.

*{is wisdom and strength}* See on Chap. 9. 4. where both these are mentioned, and attributed to God, as here.

*{he hath}* Heb. *his is.* Lest any man should think, because he said before, verse 12. that wisdom and understanding were in old men, and now in this verse, that they are with God, that therefore there is no difference between God's wisdom and man's, now he tells them that it is properly *his:* Men borrow their wisdom from him, and theirs is not worthy of the name of wisdom, in comparison of his: but God is infinitely wise of himself.

*{counsel and understanding}* The words may be taken promiscuously, to show the abundance of wisdom that is in God. Yet some distinguish them thus; God has understanding to find out all secrets and counsel, to know fit means to bring all his purposes to pass, and wisdom to order the means for attaining those ends, and strength to carry them on to execute all his will.

**V. 14. ***{Behold he breaketh down, and it cannot be built again: he shutteth up a man, and there can be no opening}* Now he proceeds to show the great wisdom and power of God, mentioned in the former verse, and that unto the end of the chapter. He not only shows God's wisdom and power to be great, but also greater than the wisdom and power of all the creatures, in that what he does, none can undo; and what he undoes, none can repair. And because men are very blockish in the things of God, he gives many high demonstrations of God's wisdom and power to convince those that slight the course of God's ordinary providence.

*{Behold}* Or, *If.* See on Chap. 9. 12. & 11. 10.

*{he breaketh down}* He destroys cities, castles, houses, provinces, as in the flood, and destruction of Sodom, and of the tower of Babel. It may be understood also of destruction of families, which are compared to buildings, Exod. 1. 21. Thus God had broken down Job's family, by destroying his children, which are in the Hebrew phrase, *builders of the house,* Gen. 16. 2. & 30. 3. Ruth 4. 10, 11.

*{and it cannot be built again}* It cannot be repaired. But this is to be understood of those things that God purposes utterly to destroy; for houses and churches destroyed by thunder and lightning, have been built again. For *Build,* see on Chap. 20. 19.

*{he shutteth up a man}* Heb. *upon a man.* As if he were in a pit, and had a great stone rolled on the mouth of it; that he might not get out, as was on Christ's grave, Matth. 27. 66. It signifies shutting up into prison, or into some dangerous disease, as Gen. 20. 17, 18. or putting into bands; for the contrary word in the end of the verse signifies loosing, as well as opening; as verse 18.

*{and there can be no opening}* Unless he please. See the like phrase, Isa. 22. 22. Rev. 3. 7. For *Open,* see on Chap. 11. 5.

**V. 15. ***{Behold he withholdeth the waters, and they dry up: also he sendeth them out, and they overturn the earth.}* God's great power, which he had set out before by his overruling men in civil affairs, now he sets out by his powerful disposing of natural things, not as men would have them, but as he please.

*{Behold}* Or, *If,* as verse 14. & Chap. 9. 12. & 11. 10.

*{he withholdeth}* Sometimes עצר signifies keeping back a thing by force, hindering or shutting it up; as 2 Chr. 13. 20. Sometimes restraining by command, as laws restrain men from many enormities. So it imports rule, 1 Sam. 9. 17. Sometimes to stop the course of a thing by prayer, as 2 Sam. 24. 21. Here it is taken in the first sense. So God by his power restrains the waters.

*{the waters}* Some understand it of keeping the rivers within their bounds, but that though it may hinder them from hurting the earth, yet it makes them not to dry up. Others, and more rightly, interpret it of rainwater, which God withholds when he keeps it in the clouds, and will not let it fall down to water the earth. See an example, 1 King. 17. 1. &c. Gen. 8. 2. For *Water,* see on Chap. 14. 9.

*{and they dry up}* Some understand it of the waters themselves, as Gen. 8. 2. Nah. 1. 4. Zech. 10. 11. Others, of the trees and herbs, which decay for want of water. Such things as live by moisture, must needs dry up, if rain fail, 1 King. 18. 5. Jer. 12. 4. Joel 1. 18. It may be understood the first way. And then it does the better illustrate, and more fully set forth the great power of God. He can keep the rain back so long, till the very rivers be dry. So it answers to the opposition, *They overturn the earth.* And the second must needs follow upon it: If rivers be dry, herbs and plants will decay. The word יבש signifies, 1. To be dried up, Joel 1. 20. 2. To be ashamed, Hos. 10. 6. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{also he sendeth them out}* It is used sometimes for sending a messenger with some errand, or command, to do something. As Joseph sent his brethren to fetch his father, Gen. 45. 24. So God gives the water a commission to overflow the earth, Psal. 147. 18. But the word has a further emphasis, and signifies a giving liberty to the waters, that before were, as it were, imprisoned in the clouds, to break through them, and fall upon the earth. So Noah sent the dove out of the ark, Gen. 8. 12. The Heb. word שלח is used. 1. For sending a messenger, Gen. 19. 13. 2. For putting away a wife by divorce, Deut. 24. 1. 3. For trees putting forth branches, Psal. 80. 11. or roots, Jer. 17. 8. 4. For letting the hair grow long, Ezek. 44. 20. 5. For setting a place on fire, Hos. 8. 14. Here it is taken in the first sense. God sends the waters out as his messengers to overturn the earth. For *Earth,* see on Chap. 9. 24.

*{and they overturn the earth}* They do change the face of the earth; for the word signifies turning, as well as overturning. See Levit. 13. 3. The earth was dry, firm, green before; now, after a deluge, it is moist, soft, ill colored. Or, they overturn houses and cities, and trees that are on the earth. See on verse 8. See an example in the flood, Gen. 7. 11. &c.

**V. 16. ***{With him is strength and wisdom: the deceived and the deceiver are his}* Job repeats what he had said before, verse 13. of God's wisdom and power, though in other words in the original, and in another order. This he does, because he had other things to speak of God's wisdom and power, and intended to set them forth, by his ordering men's manners, both good and bad. A thing that might seem strange to his friends. And therefore Job, by way of exclamation, cries out the second time, that power and wisdom is with God, to show how much he was affected with it himself, and how earnestly he desired his friends might be more affected with it.

*{with him}* See on verse 13.

*{is strength and wisdom}* Some think the same things to be intended, that were set out by other words in the original, verse 13. Others conceive some difference between them: and think that עז, signifies God's prevailing power, that cannot be resisted. גבורה: God's establishing power, whereby he upholds all things. חכמה: God's wisdom, in decreeing things. תושיה: in execution. For the last word, see on Chap. 5. 12. & 6. 13. Strength and wisdom are both joined together, to show, that God can do whatsoever he will. For some things man can do by strength: others, that cannot be effected by force are comprehended by policy. God has both these, and therefore can do anything. For *Wisdom,* see on Chap. 26. 3. on *The thing as it is.*

*{the deceived and the deceiver are his}* He does not mean, that they are both God's by creation: that suits not with his scope. But they are both subject to his authority. He could by his wisdom and power, if he pleased, keep the one from deceiving, and the other from being deceived, and that both in religious and civil affairs. And therefore he does, as it were, countenance them, in that he does not hinder them when he may. This suits with that, which Job went about to prove. See on verse 7.

**V. 17. ***{He leadeth counsellors away spoiled, and maketh the Judges fools}* He had before showed how God overrules men in things civil and moral; now he intimates, how God overrules in things political, and ordering kingdoms and commonwealths, wherein are many confusions, and he instances in counselors and judges, and kings, the chief pillars of a state; and all to show, what he undertook before to prove against his friends, that wicked men do often thrive, when good men are kept low. And that appears, because all along, from the fourteenth verse to the end, all his instances are of ruining men and states, but none at all of blessing them.

*{He leadeth away}* Heb. *He maketh to walk.*

*{counsellors}* Wise men that give advice in public affairs, to kings or rulers, as being best able to order things present, and prevent mischiefs to come. That sit at the stern of the state to guide it.

*{Spoiled}* Of their wisdom, so that they give bad counsel, to the ruin of themselves and others. Or, spoiled of the honor and wealth they have gotten. For such eminent men are usually rich. See verse 19. God defeats their counsels, and leads them away, like captives, in triumph, having deprived them of wit, wealth, and honor, 2 Sam. 15. 31. & 17. 14, 23. Isai. 19. 11. 1 Cor. 1. 19.

*{and maketh the Judges}* Judges differ from counselors, for they sit in judgment, to determine causes between man and man, according to the law. These should be wise to do right, but God often makes them fools, and then they do much wrong.

*{fools}* Or, *mad:* as Eccl. 7. 7. Isai. 44. 25. God will deprive them of understanding, or so go beyond them, that he will cross all their purposes.

**V. 18. ***{He looseth the bond of Kings, and girdeth their loins with a girdle}* Now he goes higher, and shows that God, not only overrules counselors and judges, but also disposes as he pleases of the supremest magistrates, as kings and emperors. Three things uphold their greatness, the wisdom of their counselors, authority of their judges, and the majesty of their own persons. All these God can pull down. So much is intimated in the former verse, and this.

*{He looseth}* Heb. *openeth.* For bonds are tied with knots, or buckles, which when they are opened or untied, the bond is loose, and falls off. It will hold the things, or persons bound no longer.

*{the bond of Kings}* The Chaldee paraphrast takes it for chains. As if the meaning were, God takes them out of prison, and sets them on the throne, 2 King. 25. 27. Others understand it of those, whom kings have imprisoned; God sets them at liberty, and brings the kings, their imprisoners, into their room. Others, of taking them away by some great judgments, whereby their subjects are set at liberty from the yoke of obedience. But it is rather to be understood of God's taking away that honor and authority they had in the hearts of their subjects, whereby their people are kept in obedience; which being loosed by God, people shake off all obedience to their rulers. And indeed, it is a wonder that so many men of so many different minds, states and degrees, should submit to the government of one man. And on the contrary, that being once brought under, and kingly authority settled, and upheld by wise counselors and prudent judges, the people should dare to cast it off. But both are of God, who ties the bond as fast as he please, and looses it when he please, Psal. 2. 3. Dan. 2. 21. 1 Sam. 2. 4. Psal. 18. 33, 40. Chap. 30. 11. Isai. 45. 1, 5.

*{and girdeth their loins with a girdle}* Some understand it of giving authority and power, and strength to kings, which is set out sometimes, in Scripture, by this similitude of a girdle, as Isai. 11. 5. Jer. 13. 1, 7. So soldiers going to battle, to use their strength, have their loins girt, Eph. 6. 14. But this cannot well agree with Job's scope, to set out God's judgments upon great ones to bring them down, not his mercy to establish them. Others, therefore take it, of bringing kings into a servile condition, that they which were attended by great ones before, now should wait upon others. For servants are girt as well as soldiers, that they may be ready for their work or attendance. So the Persian kings gave girdles to those they had conquered, and made them attend upon them, Luke 12. 37. & 17. 7, 8. John 13. 4. 1 Pet. 1. 13. Lastly, others bring it yet lower; God brings kings from the throne to the prison, to be bound with chains as with a girdle. So the word אסר girding, or binding is used, Psal. 118. 27. & 105. 22. Hos. 10. 10. Job 21. 18. 2 King. 25. 7. This is laying affliction upon men's loins, Psal. 66. 11. And this agrees best with Job's scope, mentioned before. The word אסר signifies. 1. To bind, or tie, 1 Sam. 6. 7. 2. To bind from a thing, Numb. 11. 38. 3. To bind to a duty or oblige. Num. 30. 2. Here it is taken in the first sense.

**V. 19. ***{He leadeth Princes away spoiled, and overthroweth the mighty}* Having showed God's judgments on kings, he now comes to show it on princes, and men of great estates, who are next to them.

*{He leadeth away}* See on verse 17.

*{Princes}* The word כהן most commonly signifies priests, in Scripture: but it is used also for princes, and such as are about kings, and next in honor to them, as 2 Sam. 8. 18. with 1 Chr. 18. 17. 2 Sam. 20. 26. and Joseph's father-in-law, Gen. 41. 45. and Moses his father-in-law, Exod. 2. 16. are thought to be temporal rulers of those places, for it is not likely, that they would marry the daughters of idolatrous priests. The word came thus to be used in both senses, because the same persons at first, were both priests and rulers, as the firstborn before the law. Moses under the law, Psal. 99. 6. Eli and Samuel. And the priests were then attending upon God, as princes attend upon kings. But here it is used for princes, because it is most likely, that Job lived before the priesthood was settled in Aaron's family, and therefore he offers sacrifice for his children, Chap. 1. and is commanded by God to do it for his friends, Chap. 42. Whereas Saul, after the priesthood established, lost his kingdom for such an act, 1 Sam. 13. 13, 14. Of Job's time, see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{Spoiled}* See on verse 17.

*{and overthroweth}* This word imports an utter destruction, as of a tree that is plucked up by the roots, Prov. 21. 12. & 22. 12.

*{the mighty}* Such as are potent men in a kingdom, and strongly seated as it were on a rock, and think none can remove them, Num. 24. 21. But think they can carry all before them, like the stream of mighty rivers, and none can withstand them, Amos 5. 24. He may mean, either such as are mighty in a state, or strong warriors. The word איתן signifies 1. Strong Num. 24. 21. 2. Strength, Exod. 14. 27. Here it is used in the first sense.

**V. 20. ***{He removeth away the speech of the trusty, and taketh away the understanding of the aged}* Skillful orators, and ancient wise men, are a great stay to kingdoms, if publicly employed: and if not, yet such men think they can save themselves and their families in troublous times. But God can pull them down also.

*{He removeth away}* The word properly signifies to take a thing out of its place, and so by consequence, taking a thing quite away: for things removed, are not to be found where they were.

*{the speech} The lip.* Because the lip is the instrument of speaking. See Chap. 2. 10. So one language, is called one lip, Gen. 11. 1. Hereby is meant, not making them dumb, but taking away that extraordinary faculty they have of speaking understandingly, or eloquently. For *Lip,* see on Chap. 15. 6.

*{of the trusty}* The word נאמן is by some translated *eloquent,* from נאם, *to speak.* And such men are of great use in a state. By others, *such as speak truly.* For such will rightly inform in public affairs. By others, *the confident.* For they that speak truly and elegantly, as they are of great use in the public, so they may well be confident in what they advise, and look to be believed. Such was Ahithophel's counsel, 2 Sam. 16. 23. By others, *the trusty:* as in our translation; for such men are most worthy to be trusted, both by governors and people, in places of greatest consequence, who can speak well, and will speak truly and confidently; as becomes orators, lawyers, ambassadors. And in these three last senses, the word is taken from אמן: which in some conjugations, signifies to be true of speech, faithful, confident. Such God can take away, or take those qualities from them, Isai. 3. 3. The word is translated *faithful,* Prov. 25. 13.

*{and taketh away}* Heb. *taketh.* For in his, He gives, and he takes, Job 1. 21. And because the Hebrews have no compounds, it is used also to take away: as 1 King. 19. 14. Hos. 4. 11. An exposition of the former word, *removing.* For *Taketh,* see on *Carryeth,* Chap. 15. 12.

*{the understanding}* Heb. *the sense,* or, *savour,* or, *taste.* As it is translated, verse 11. And Psal. 34. 8. And by a metaphor, it is translated to the mind, and used for the understanding, whereby a man knows what is true or false, as by the palate, what is sweet or sour. Thus it is used, Psal. 119. 66. Prov. 11. 22. & 31. 18. 1 Sam. 21. 13. Psal. 34. in the title. For *Understanding,* see on Chap. 6. 6. on *Taste.*

*{of the aged}* It signifies often men aged in years, and because such have, or should have most understanding (as verse 12. Chap. 32. 9.) it is sometimes used for men of great understanding, although not old in years; and so it is translated senators, Psal. 105. 22. These also God can take away, and does, when he intends to ruin a kingdom; as Isa. 3. 2, 3, 4. The word זקן signifies. 1. An old man, Gen. 25. 8. 2. A ruler, 2 King. 10. 1. For age brings experience, and makes men fit to rule others. And the words answerable in Greek and Latin signify both these. Here it is used in the first sense.

**V. 21. ***{He poureth contempt upon Princes, and weakeneth the strength of the mighty.}* He goes on to give us more signs of God's ruining kingdoms, by taking away honor from princes, and strength from mighty men, which were their peculiar ornaments, and whereby they did most further the public good.

*{He poureth}* The beginning of this verse with the end of the 24. verse, make up one complete verse, Psal. 107. 40. which makes some think, that Job took it out of David, and so was of later time than he. But why might not David take it out of Job? And the rather, because he puts them together, that are scattered in Job. Or, Why might not the Spirit of God use the same words by divers men, though they saw not one another write? The word *pouring,* argues abundance; as Isa. 44. 3. Zech. 12. 10. Acts 2. 17. God will lay abundance of shame and scorn upon them.

*{contempt}* Makes them to be despised, that were highly honored before. So the word is used, verse 5. Chap. 31. 34.

*{upon Princes}* The word נדיב signifies such as are free and bountiful, and so are in great esteem among the people, like princes, although they have no authority over them. No marvel, if mean men, that cannot do good to others; or great men, that will not, be contemned. But it is a wonder that even great and bountiful should be despised, and it is a forerunner of the destruction of a kingdom. For the word, see Exod. 35. 5. Psal. 83. 11. Prov. 19. 6. Luke 22. 25. For the thing itself, see Dan. 2. 21. Men are not so high and honorable, but God can make them as low and contemptible.

*{and weakeneth}* Makes them remiss, or loose, Jer. 38. 4.

*{the strength}* Heb. *מזיח the girdle.* Men girt, are more ready to fight, than men ungirt, and much of men's strength lies in their loins; and therefore girding the loins, is taken for exercising strength; and ungirding, or loosing them, for inability, and weakness, Dan. 5. 6. In those Eastern countries where their garments were long, they used to gird them, when they went to put out their strength in labor or battle, 1 King. 20. 11. For the word, see Psal. 109. 19. Isa. 23. 10.

*{of the mighty}* Valiant soldiers, and men fit for war, which are very needful to uphold a kingdom. And when such are weakened, a kingdom is soon overrun by enemies. The word signifies *rivers* and *torrents,* which run with a mighty force, and bear down all before them. So do soldiers in war. See 2 Sam. 22. 16. Psal. 42. 1. Job 6. 15.

**V. 22. ***{He discovereth deep things out of darkness, and bringeth out to light the shadow of death.}* Having formerly given great demonstrations of God's power, now he sets out his infinite wisdom by many strange effects of it.

*{He discovereth}* The word signifies, to unfold a thing, and lay it open to the sight of men, which was hid from them before; and that by bringing it out of darkness.

*{deep things}* Hidden things, that are as far from men's understanding and knowledge, as things deep buried in the earth, are from men's eyes. It may be understood of prophecies of times to come, conspiracies against princes; but especially of the privy counsels, and deep reaches of kings to maintain their authority, which God often by his wisdom brings to light. For Job goes on in setting out the ruins of kingdoms.

*{out of darkness}* Deep things lie in dark holes unseen of men: so do counsels of great men; but God can bring them forth to the light; as follows in this verse.

*{and bringeth out to light}* This shows the manner of revealing them, namely by taking them out of their dark holes, and setting them in the light for all men to see. For *Light,* see on Chap. 17. 11.

*{the shadow of death}* Those things that are as it were buried in the grave, and hid in eternal darkness, which no man hoped or expected ever to have seen brought to light in this world. For such strange discoveries, see Chap. 28. 3. & 34. 22. 2 King. 6. 11, 12. Mat. 10. 26. Dan. 2. 22. 1 Cor. 2. 10. & 4. 5. For the phrases of deep things, darkness, and shadow of death, see Chap. 3. 5. & 10. 22. Prov. 9. 18. Rev. 2. 24. Psal. 107. 10, 14.

**V. 23. ***{He increaseth the nations, and destroyeth them: he enlargeth the nations, and straiteneth them again.}* God not only ruins great and wise men, but also whole nations, when he pleases.

*{He increaseth}* God increases nations in number of people; as he did before, and after the flood; and in the land of Canaan, Psal. 107. 38. Also in wealth, as he promises to deal with his obedient people, Deut. 28. 5, 6. &c. Lastly, in honor and reputation, Deut. 28. 12, 13.

*{the nations}* The word גוים is sometimes used for the Gentiles, and idolatrous people; as Psal. 2. 1. & 79. 1. But here it may be taken for any, whom God does multiply, be they good or bad.

*{and destroyeth them}* Or, *that he may destroy them.* So this particle is used, Chap. 13. 13. For Job's intention is to make good, what he undertook before, that afflictions may light on good men, as well as bad. This God does sometimes secretly as a moth, and sometimes openly, as a lion, Hos. 5. 12, 14. It may be understood of increasing one nation to destroy another, as the Israelites to ruin the Canaanites. But it is better understood of the same nations raised by God, that they may have the greater fall, Exod. 9. 16.

*{he enlargeth the Nations}* As they do increase in number, so he does enlarge their borders, and spreads them abroad upon the face of the earth, that all may live, and not one be a burden to another. So he did after the flood, Gen. 10.

*{and straiteneth them again}* Heb. *leadeth them.* Now because the Hebrews have no compounds, it may signify *leading in,* or *leading back;* as the shepherd in the morning leads his sheep out into a large pasture, and at night brings them into a narrow pinfold. So God after that he has enlarged a people in the day of their prosperity, brings them to straits in a night of adversity. And it may be read, *That he may straiten them again;* as before in this verse. The word נחה signifies. 1. To lead, Psal. 8. 2. To put, or place, 2 King. 18. 11. Here it is used in the first sense.

**V. 24. ***{He taketh away the heart of the chief of the people of the earth, and causeth them to wander in a wilderness, where there is no way.}* Job now in the conclusion of this chapter, sets down the way, how God does ruin nations, by frustrating the counsels of the governors, whereby both themselves, and the people under them, come to destruction.

*{he taketh}* Heb. *he removeth.* See on verse 20.

*{the heart}* The understanding, so that he cannot give good counsel for the preservation of the state. See on verse 3. and verse 17. For *Heart,* see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{of the chief}* Heb. *of the heads.* Men set over others to guide, and direct them, as the head does all the members of the body; as Exod. 6. 14. Num. 1. 16. Deut. 1. 13. Judg. 11. 8. &c. For *Head,* see on Chap. 19. 9. and 20. 16. on *Poison.*

*{of the people of the earth}* Of the common people, who are to be guided by their heads and rulers. For *Earth,* see on Chap. 9. 24.

*{and causeth them to wander}* See Psal. 107. 40. Gen. 20. 13. Makes them for want of understanding to give foolish counsel, to mislead themselves and others. See verse 16. Psal. 119. 176. Isa. 63. 17.

*{in a wilderness}* Or, *into a wildernesse.* The word signifies such a confused estate, that they know not which way to turn. Like to the dark chaos, where no light was, Gen. 1. 2.

*{where there is no way}* Not into a place, where many ways are, and it is hard to descry the right, but into such a wilderness, where there are no steps of any way at all to be seen, which is much worse. To a place, where there is no path directing to any house, or town, or place of safety. So base rulers are so puzzled with strange events, beyond expectation, threatening ruin to them, and their people, that they are as it were in a wilderness, not knowing which way to expedite themselves or theirs. For *Way,* see on Chap. 17. 9.

**V. 25. ***{They grope in the dark without light, and he maketh them to stagger like a drunken man.}* The inability of these heads of the people, to extricate themselves, or theirs out of trouble, is set out further by two similitudes; one, of a man groping in the dark for want of light; another, of a drunken man staggering for the lightness of his head.

*{they grope in the dark}* Men that cannot see their way must feel for it. So must blind men at noondays, Job 5. 14. Gen. 19. 11. and seeing men in the dark night, Isa. 59. 10.

*{without light}* Where there is not the least beam, or spark, of light to direct them. The doubling the thing in other words increases it, Amos 5. 18. Joh. 1. 3, 20. Isa. 38. 1. The meaning is, that these rulers try for ways to help themselves and their people out of misery, but can find none at all.

*{and he maketh them to stagger}* Heb. *to err,* or *to wander;* as verse 24.

*{like a drunken man}* As he is uncertain in his gate, leaning first to this side, then to that, so are they. First they try one way, and then another, but none will release them. Their heart is intoxicated with folly, as the drunkard's with wine, so that they know not what they do. See Isa. 19. 14. & 24. 20. Psal. 107. 27.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1. ***{Lo, mine eye hath seen all this, mine ear hath heard and understood it.}* What Job had formerly declared in the chapter before, now he confirms by the testimony of two principal senses most used in getting of knowledge; to wit, seeing and hearing.

*{Lo}* O, *Behold.* A word usually put before great matters in Scripture, to procure attendance to them. See Chap. 1. verse 12.

*{mine eye hath seen all this}* The eye is the instrument of seeing, and the soul receives much light by the eye. It observes many things in the world, which the eye presents unto it. So had Job, by his own observation, collected what he spoke before of good and bad men's outward condition in the world. He begins with the eye, as the surer sense. We say, one eye-witness is better than ten ear-witnesses; and so is his testimony in legal trials. Job therefore goes a sure way, that speaks what he saw. For this kind of proof, and the certainty of it, see 1 Joh. 1. 1, 3. Acts 1. 21, 22. & 10. 39, 40, 41. For the phrase, see Job 7. 8. & 10. 18. For *Eye,* see on Chap. 2. 12. For *Seen,* look on Chap. 19. 27. on *Behold.*

*{mine ear hath heard}* Lest his friends should think it was but his own fancy, he calls in others to bear witness with him. He had heard as much from godly and wise men, who had observed the same as well as he, Chap. 12. 11, 12. Chap. 8. verse 8. For *Ear,* see on Chap. 15. 21. For *Heard,* see on Chap. 15. 17.

*{and understood it}* This he adds, to take away that which his friends might object. If they should say, you heard, it may be, wise men's words, but misunderstood them. Nay, says he, I understood them well, and rightly. All this is to show, that he was fully assured of the truth of what he had delivered, though his friends contradicted it.

**V. 2. ***{What ye know, the same do I know also: I am not inferior unto you.}* Job does not in these words confirm the truth of all his friends had said before; but rather intimates, that whereas they thought themselves more skillful in the course of God's providence than he, and able to instruct him therein, he knew as much in that way, as they; nay more. See on Chap. 12. 3.

*{What ye know, the same do I know also.}* Or, rather thus, *I also know as much as you.* The Hebrew words are, *According to your knowledge I also know.*

*{I am not inferior unto you.}* See on Chap. 12. 3. where these very words are, and are in the notes expounded. It seems by Job's repeating them so soon, that his friends had a very high opinion of their own knowledge, and a very low one of Job's.

**V. 3. ***{Surely, I would speak to the Almighty, and I desire to reason with God.}* In this verse Job shows himself weary of reasoning with his friends, and that he had rather deal with God than with them, and so joins with Zophar in his wish, Chap. 11. 5.

*{Surely}* A word of assurance. I do earnestly and seriously desire it, Chap. 12. 7. & 1. 11.

*{I would speak}* I would plead with God in a judiciary way. So the word *speaking,* is used, Psal. 51. 4. for speaking in judgment, as it is there expounded.

*{to the Almighty}* For this name of God, see Chap. 5. 17. & 6. 4, 14. & 8. 3, 5. & 11. 7. Some understand it of pleading before God, as a judge between him, and his friends, because they would accuse Job as much as they could. But it appears by many arguments, that Job intended to plead with God, as with a party that had dealt too hardly with him, and countenanced his friends against him. For 1. He desires not to speak to his friends in God's presence, but to the Almighty. 2. He desires to speak by way of reasoning with God himself; not with them before God. 3. His plea afterwards proves it. It is a maintaining of his own cause, verse 15. And he pleads directly with God; not with his friends before God, verses 21, 22, 23, 24.

*{and I desire}* It is a thing I much wish for, and would take great pleasure in, because I know I could clear my own innocency, and show myself to be no hypocrite, as you take me to be, and as God seems to do by his so rough handling of me, verse 22, 24. So the word is translated, *Delight,* Num. 14. 8. Psal. 40. 8. For *Desire,* see on 9. 3. on *Will,* and on Chap. 22. 3. on *Pleasure.*

*{to reason with}* To plead my cause, by bringing reasons to clear my innocency, and answering objections made by God against it, as the manner of disputing is. I would God would leave beating me, and would dispute the cause with me. This boldness is that, which both Elihu and God blame Job for in the end of the book, though neither of them condemn him for an hypocrite: and that shows, that Job did speak amiss of God in his passion, and is not altogether to be excused, much less in everything to be commended. For *Reason,* see on Chap. 15. 3.

*{God}* For this name of God, see Chap. 5. 8. & 8. 5, 13, 20. & 9. 2. & 12. 6.

**V. 4. ***{But ye are forgers of lies, ye are all Physicians of no value.}* In this verse Job shows that his friends came with a purpose to comfort him in his miseries, as physicians come to heal sick people; but they did him no good, because they judged amiss of his disease, and used corrosives instead of cordials.

*{But}* This word argues an opposition, either between himself and his friends, as if he spoke the truth, but they uttered lies; or else between God, and his friends, attributing truth to God, and falsehood to his friends. It may be translated *For;* as Chap. 11. 4. And then it includes a reason, why Job will reason no more with his friends, but rather with God, because God though he be angry, yet will hear and speak truth, and will be appeased: but his friends would not. In the Hebrew it is, *But surely.* The same word that is used in the beginning of the 3. verse, and is omitted also Chap. 12. 7. See on Chap. 1. 11.

*{ye are}* It appears here, as also verse 2. that Job speaks not to Zophar alone, who spoke last, but to all his three friends, who had now all spoken against him: and therefore he might well take liberty to confute other men's speeches as well as Zophar's, especially such things as he had not answered before.

*{forgers}* So it is translated, but the word טפל, never signifies so, but חרש, is used for to invent lies, as a workman frames a building. The word is used but twice more in Scripture; and it signifies *sowing,* whereby one piece of cloth is sowed to another; as Chap. 14. 17. Some take it as an accusation of his friends, that they had as it were sowed one lie to another to disgrace him, that is, had multiplied many lies against him. But it rather intimates fastening lies upon God or him, as one piece of cloth is fastened upon another: As it should be translated, Psal. 119. 69. The proud have sowed, or, fastened a lie upon me. He accuses his friends that they did go about to wrong God and him, by speaking falsely of both.

*{of lies}* Heb. *of a lye.* For the word has a plural in the original. And so it is translated singularly, Psal. 119. 69. And as it is likely David did intend some singular slander of him there, so Job here intends that main accusation of him, whereby they accused him for an hypocrite, because they could not see how God should be just else, that dealt more severely with him, than ever he did with any that feared him, and so by one false opinion wronged both God and Job. This God charges them withal, Chap. 42. 8.

*{ye are all Physicians of no value}* You are every one of you like unskillful physicians, that not knowing the disease aright, do either give things hurtful, or misapply good things, and so instead of healing him, make the sick man worse. So do you to me. You are miserable comforters, Chap. 16. 2. You pretend that you come to comfort me, and whereas you knowing my piety, should uphold my spirits in my affliction with God's promises, you add affliction to affliction, telling me, that God lies load on me for my impiety. Some read it, *Idol Physicians,* as the idol shepherd, Zech. 11. 17. Taking no more care of Job, than ministers of their flocks, or idols of their worshippers. *Physicians.* Heb. *Healers.* The word רפא signifies. 1. To heal the body, Deut. 32. 39. 2. To heal the soul by forgiveness of sins, Psal. 41. 4. 3. Mending things out of order, Psal. 60. 2. Here it is used in the second sense, for they were not professed physicians; Neither did Job look for cure of his body from them, but comfort for his soul, which yet they would not afford him, but rather discouraged him.

**V. 5. ***{O that you would altogether hold your peace, and it should be your wisdom.}* Job finding no comfort in the words of his friends, but much discouragement, persuades them, in this verse, to save a labor, and be silent; and in the next, to give him audience, that they may see their errors.

*{O that you}* Heb. *who will give that you,* &c. See on Chap. 6. 8. & 11. 5.

*{would altogether hold your peace}* Heb. *in being silent would be silent.* The word החריש signifies, 1. To dig the ground, or plow it, as Deut. 22. 10. 2. By a metaphor, to think, or devise; for actions are prepared by thoughts, as the ground by plowing, is fitted to receive the seed. So it is used, Chap. 4. 8. Prov. 3. 29. 3. By an antiphrasis or contrariety, (which is frequent in the Hebrew tongue, for the same word to signify contraries for want of compounds, whereby they are set out in Greek and Latin) it signifies to say or do nothing, to be idle or silent, as Gen. 34. 5. And so it is taken here. The doubling of the word in the original, adds to the signification; as dying the death, Gen. 2. 17. He would not have them speak a word more in this business. It may be he alludes to Zophar's speech, Chap. 11. 3. as if he had said, You would have me to hold my peace, but you had more need to do it yourselves, for you speak more foolishly than I. Or to their former silence, Chap. 2. 13. You had done well, if you had kept your former silence, for then had you not discomfited me, nor spoken so many falsehoods concerning God's proceedings.

*{and it should be your wisdom}* Heb. *and it should be to you for wisdom.* There neither is, nor appears wisdom in silence properly, but in speech. It may be taken comparatively; You would show more wisdom, that is, less folly; for you would not discover the vanity of your hearts, if you did hold your peace. So the *foolishnesse of God,* 1 Cor. 1. 25. is put for that action, wherein God least sets out his wisdom; for there is no folly in God. Or it may be meant in regard of other men's apprehension. Men would in charity judge you to be wise, if your own words did not bewray your folly. Suitable to Prov. 17. 28.

**V. 6. ***{Hear now my reasoning, and hearken to the pleadings of my lips.}* He does not bid them be gone, though he bid them be silent: but would have them to stay, and not be ashamed to learn the truth from him. Yet with reproof of their ill pleading for God, he mingles passionate and distempered speeches; as verse 13, 14. &c. So easy is it for us, especially in affliction, to show our own passion, where we have just cause to reprove others. As it is decent for you to hide your own folly by silence, so it is profitable for you to learn, by hearkening to me. Forasmuch as I have heard your accusations patiently, I beseech you give me the hearing while I go about to disprove them, and confute them, and see what arguments I can bring in defense of my own cause, and to set out your errors.

*{Hear}* It is not a command, for Job had no power to command their audience; but an earnest and vehement persuasion to them to give audience to him. For *Hear,* see on verse 17.

*{now}* At present. Defer it not. Or, *I pray you.* For so the word sometimes is translated, as Chap. 8. 8. For *Now,* see on Chap. 4. 7. on *I pray thee.*

*{my reasoning}* What I have to say by way of argument and disputation, either to defend myself, or to accuse you. For the word includes both disputation, and reprehension. And Job does both, in the residue of this chapter, sometimes accusing his friends, and sometimes defending himself. The same word was used verse 3. and is Chap. 23. 4. The Heb. word תוכחה signifies. 1. Reproving, or rebuking, Hos. 5. 9. 2. Reasoning by arguments, Chap. 23. 4. 3. Correction, Psal. 39. 11. Here it is taken in the second sense for *Reasoning.*

*{and hearken}* To hearken, is more than to hear. A man may hear a thing by accident, but he hearkens to it on purpose, as scholars do to their master's instruction. Job expects not a superficial hearing from his friends, but a serious attention both with ear and mind.

*{to the pleadings of my lips}* The word *pleadings,* in the original, comes from a word that signifies to contend, or chide. For when men plead for themselves, or against others, they can hardly forbear angry words. For the word *lips,* see Chap. 2. 10. & 8. 21. & 11. 5. For *Lips,* see on Chap. 15. 6.

**V. 7. ***{Will you speak wickedly for God? and talk deceitfully for him?}* Here Job begins to answer the sayings of his friends, and to confute those arguments wherewith they pleaded against him. He compares them to corrupt lawyers, that pleading for some wealthy or potent client, care not what they say to oppress others, be it right or wrong, so they may carry the cause for their client, and procure or retain his favor. So Job charges his friends here, that they did not look into the cause itself, nor search what was right and true, else they needed not to have made him an hypocrite to clear God. And yet this overmuch care would not be pleasing to God neither. So well can we speak in reproving others, but unadvisedly in our own sorrows.

*{will you}* The question gives an emphasis to it. Dare you to speak wickedly though it were for God himself? Or, *ought you,* or, *should you.* Does this become you? So the future is translated, 2 Chr. 19. 2. Do you think you do well to do so? Can you be so blind?

*{speak wickedly}* Heb. *Speak iniquity;* as Psal. 7. 3. Or, *unrighteousnesse,* as Lev. 19. 15. The word signifies injury in or out of judgment. Will you condemn me unjustly, to clear God? See for the word, Chap. 6. 29, 30. & 11. 14.

*{for God}* In God's behalf. So this particle is used, Judg. 6. 31. Will you plead for Baal? Some read it, *of God.* That is, concerning his proceedings. Others, *to God.* But Job's friends did not direct their speech to God, but to Job; neither did they so much speak evil of God, as for God to injure Job. The next verse will clear it. For they that accept God's person, do not speak evil of him, but of others for his sake. Neither is it good sense to say, Will you contend of him, or to him; but for him.

*{and talk deceitfully for him?}* The word רמה signifies literally to shoot, or cast down; as Exod. 15. 1. Psal. 78. 9, 57. and metaphorically to deceive, as Gen. 29. 25. For they that are cast down by deceit, are shot at with calumnies, and cast down from their hope. In these words he doubles the charge laid against his friends in the beginning of the verse, and particularizes it, declaring what iniquity he charged them withal, namely, fraud or deceit. The thing he aims at was this, That they dealt very deceitfully with him, in that they went about to prove him an hypocrite, that they might clear God's justice in laying so great plagues upon him. He counts them flatterers of God, Chap. 17. 5. & 32. 21. & 36. 3, 4.

**V. 8. ***{Will ye accept his person? will ye contend for God?}* In this verse he shows the cause, why his friends were so forward to condemn him to please God, as they imagined, to wit, because they accept God's person, and looked not at the equity of Job's cause, and that made them plead so earnestly against him.

*{Will ye accept his person?}* Heb. *will ye take,* or *lift up, his face?* Some understand it thus; Will you take God's person upon you, and be his deputies to plead for him against me, as if you represented his person? Others thus, Do you take upon you to be gods, that you judge my heart, and pronounce me an hypocrite? Other thus, Will you take upon you to plead God's cause, so as he may lift up his face with boldness, as one that has the better of me? So the phrase is used, Chap. 11. 15. Gen. 32. 20. Deut. 28. 50. Isa. 3. 3. But it is rather an allusion to judgment, wherein the judge or pleader looking too much upon the face or person of one party, (that is, upon his birth, place, riches, or friendship) pleads for, or judges for him against another, whose cause is juster. And Job condemns his friends, that they looked so much on God's greatness, that they did not weigh the equity of his cause, but condemned him for a wicked man, and that under color of justifying God, who had so sorely afflicted him. For the phrase, see verse 10. Mal. 2. 9. Lev. 19. 15. Prov. 18. 5. & 24. 23. & 28. 21. He would not have them think to gratify God with oppressing him, as flatterers use for preferment to gratify princes, by wronging the subjects. For *Accept,* see on Chap. 11. 3. on *Suffer.* For *Person,* or *Face,* see on Chap. 14. 20. on *Countenance.*

*{will ye contend for God?}* It is no fault to contend for God, but a thing commendable. Some paraphrase it thus; Do you think that God has need of your help to maintain his cause? Is he like Baal, that needs one to plead for him, Judg. 6. 31? God can plead his own cause well enough. Others knit it better to the words before; Will you in this manner contend for God by wicked and deceitful speeches, and oppressing me, to clear him? This carriage does not become you. Thus Paul was made all things to all men, that is, conformed to all men, in such things as were spoken before, 1 Cor. 9. 22. for he would not sin to please any. The word *contending,* is the same that is translated *pleading,* verse 6. See there.

**V. 9. ***{Is it good that he should search you out? or as one man mocketh another, do ye so mock him?}* Job proceeds with his charge against his friends, for wronging both God and him; and presses it with divers arguments, 1. From the unprofitableness of this course, in the beginning of this verse. 2. From the uncomeliness of it, in the end of this verse. 3. From God's reproof that would light on them, verse 10. 4. From God's majesty, verse 11. 5. From their own brittle condition, verse 12. and then concludes with a new desire of silence, and audience, verse 13.

*{Is it good}* Is it profitable for you? you look it may be for some great reward for pleading God's cause against me, but you will find it hurtful to you in the end. For the several significations of the word *good,* see on Chap. 10. 3. For the phrase, *Is it good?* that is, is it not; see the like, Gen. 18. 17. Chap. 8. 10. For the aggravation. It is not good, that is, it is very bad; see the like, Exod. 20. 7. 1 Sam. 2. 24. Psal. 51. 17. For *Good,* see on Chap. 21. 13. on *Wealth.*

*{that}* Or, *Will it be profitable for you when he shall search you out*? For so this particle signifies, Deut. 4. 25. Psal. 32. 2. Though you think you do well now, the time will come when you shall know you have done ill. So it did, Chap. 42. 8. So that Job proved herein a true prophet.

*{he should search you out}* He speaks of God after the manner of men, who take much pains to find out the truth of hidden things, especially in matters of judgment, Chap. 29. 16. Deut. 13. 14. God need not search, who knows all things. But he is said to search out a matter, when he declares by word or works that he knows it, Psal. 17. 3. & 139. 23. Job's meaning is, that God would reveal their evil intentions, and close carriage, as fully as a judge does secret murders, when he has thoroughly searched into the cause, and fully knows the truth, and then they must look for reproof from God, verse 10. if not for punishment.

*{or as one man mocketh another, will ye so mock him?}* The word התל does not signify to deride, or scoff at, as we commonly take mocking, but to delude or beguile, as Gen. 31. 7. Judg. 16. 10. The word אנוש, imports a weak or sorry man. The thing Job accuses his friends of, is not deriding of God, but dealing with him, as corrupt lawyers do with their clients, pleading their cause by bad arguments, and so making them lose the day. So they labored to maintain God's justice by defending, that God did afflict none but hypocrites in that heavy manner, as he did Job, which was an untruth; and had God's justice had no better pillars, it must have fallen to the ground. Weak men may be thus deluded by their advocates, but the all-seeing God cannot. Or it may be read thus; *Or as ye mock a man, will ye mock him?* That is, though you might so delude a weak sorry man like me, yet do not think that God can be so mocked. You will find the contrary in the end, Gal. 6. 7.

**V. 10. ***{He will surely reprove you, if ye do secretly accept persons.}* You look for some great commendation from God, for pleading his cause so stoutly: but because you do it in an ill manner, instead of thanks, you shall meet with reproof.

*{He will surely reprove you}* Heb. *In reproving he will reprove you.* That is, he will certainly do it. So this doubling the word is used, Gen. 2. 17. The word signifies reproving by words or deeds, blaming or punishing, Prov. 9. 8. Psal. 6. 1. 2 Sam. 7. 14. For *Reprove,* see on Chap. 15. 3. on *Reason.*

*{if ye do}* It does not argue a supposition, but a thing done, as Chap. 8. 4. If you go on to condemn me, as you have done already.

*{secretly}* As deceitful persons use to do, who pretend one thing, and intend another. It is the same with, *deceitfully,* verse 7. Making show, that you condemn me to honor God, although your consciences tell you, that I am not an ungodly man.

*{accept persons}* Though it be God's own person. He is so just, that he cannot endure that you should wrong me, no not to defend him. See how well we can defend God's justice against others, when we cannot see it so clearly in our own afflictions. This was Job's case at this time. He could see, how his friends wronged God's justice in censuring him for an hypocrite, because of his afflictions, but could not see how he wronged God's justice, in complaining of overmuch pressure.

**V. 11. ***{Shall not his excellency make you afraid? and his dread, fall upon you?}* Now he presses them to see their error, from the greatness of God's majesty, compared with their low condition and weakness. If the matter itself affect you not, let God's excellence do it, that may well affright such sorry creatures as you are.

*{Shall not}* A prediction of a further revelation of God's majesty, to their terror and confusion, if they repent not. Others read it, *Should not,* and then it is an intimation of their duty, showing that there was majesty enough in God, to make them deal sincerely, if they did well weigh his greatness. Others, thus, *Doth not:* and so it shows the cause why they dealt so deceitfully, to wit, because they did not cast their eyes upon God's greatness, who would not endure to be thus deluded, though weak men might.

*{his excellency}* His glorious majesty, whereby he is lifted above all men and angels. So the word is used, Chap. 31. 23.

*{make you afraid}* It signifies a great fear, or affrighting, as 2 Sam. 22. 5. Esth. 7. 6. Isa. 21. 4. God's greatness is invisible, but if it were manifested to you, it would affright you.

*{and his dread fall upon you}* The word פחד signifies a fear, arising from some outward danger. Therefore it is joined with the pit, and the snare, as intending some outward terrible object, affrighting a man, Isa. 24. 17. See more of this word, Chap. 3. 25. It is said to fall upon a man, because God does, as it were from heaven, throw down great stones in his wrath to overwhelm men, Psal. 11. 6. God is above, and his wrath falls upon us below, Rom. 1. 18. Rev. 11. 11. Pineda on the place, mentions a Spanish proverb; The hill and the stones are God's. Wherein God is compared to one that has a fort on a great hill, and stones enough to tumble down, to destroy all that oppose him. There is no resisting things that fall on us from above, nor no flying from them: whereas dangers that meet us in the face, are no higher than we, and may be resisted. Some read it, *When his dread shall fall upon you;* Then his majesty will affright you, though it do not now. So this particle is translated, 1 Sam. 13. 11. Psal. 139. 16. Others read the whole verse thus, *Shall not this acceptation of him make you afraid, seeing his dread will fall upon you?* For the word signifies to accept, as well as to lift up, and therefore may be translated acceptation, as well as excellency. It is the same word that is used for accepting of persons, verse 8, 10. And then he puts them in mind, both of their sin, and of the judgment, that should fall upon them for it.

**V. 12. ***{Your remembrances are like unto ashes, your bodies, to bodies of clay}* In this verse, he opposes their weakness to God's greatness, to affright them the more. Majesty is terrible to all, but especially to mean persons.

*{Your remembrances}* Those things for the which you are so often remembered, and mentioned by the sons of men, as your wealth, honor, strength, &c. Your monuments that you set up, and in which you pride yourselves. Or, your fame will go away and be clean forgotten: as the word is used, Prov. 10. 7.

*{are like unto}* Heb. *are parables (or similitudes) of.* That is, are very like to ashes. The word משל signifies. 1. A proverb, Prov. 1. 1. 2. A similitude, or likeness, or parable, Ezek. 16. 44. & 17. 2. So here.

*{ashes}* They are not compared to ashes, to show the vanishing of them, as ashes are scattered with every wind, but to set out the baseness of their best endowments; they are in comparison of God's excellency, but as ashes, that are trod under foot of man, as things of no value. And so it is fitly opposed to God's excellency spoken of verse 11. This word אפר is used in Scripture language. 1. To set out the frailty, and vileness of our bodies, Gen. 18. 27. 2. Sorrow, 2 Sam. 13. 19. 3. Vain labor, Isa. 44. 20. Here it is used to the first end mentioned.

*{your bodies}* The word signifies anything, that is higher than other things joined to it. As the highest place of the altar, Ezek. 43. 13. the eyebrows, Levit. 14. 9. And it is put for the whole body of a man, because it stands upright, and is higher than the bodies of beasts. For *Bodies,* see on Chap. 15. 26. on *Bosses.*

*{to}* Are like to bodies of clay. The word of likeness, is to be taken out of the beginning of the verse.

*{bodies of clay}* Like to such images of men, as are made of clay or earth. They are of no value, how highly soever you think of yourselves. So the word is used, to set out a base matter, Chap. 4. 19. & 10. 9. Exod. 1. 14. Isa. 64. 8. and it is fitly opposed to God's excellency, verse 11. For *Clay,* see on Chap. 4. 19.

**V. 13. ***{Hold your peace, let me alone that I may speak, and let come on me what will.}* Job having set before his friends God's excellency, and their own baseness to humble them, now requires what he wished for before, verse 5. that they would be silent.

*{Hold your peace}* Do not interrupt me, but hear me quietly, as I have heard you. Heb. *be silent:* as 1 Sam. 7. 8. Psal. 28. 1. Jer. 38. 27. For *Hold your peace,* see on Chap. 11. 3. and on verse 5. of this chap.

*{let me alone}* Heb. *from me.* There wants the word *cease,* which is expressed, Chap. 7. 16. Cease from me, in the original: Let me alone, in the translation. The meaning is, Let me speak alone, till I have fully eased my mind, and have said all I have to say for myself. Seeing your words neither comfort me, nor profit me, nor are right in themselves, let me speak, and be you silent awhile. It may be they began to take him off, as if he had spoken impertinently, and therefore he again presses them to audience.

*{that I may speak}* That I may declare my mind freely, and fully.

*{and let come on me}* Heb. *let passe on me.* To wit, by way of punishment, as 2 King. 16. 3. 2 Sam. 12. 31. For *Pass,* see on Chap. 15. 19.

*{what will}* I must speak, whatever become of me: and what I do, I will do at my own peril. I hope God will be more favorable to me than you are. But if God should, for my speaking, lay more troubles upon me, yet would I not cease to maintain, that I am no wicked man. So 2 Sam. 18. 23. *But howsoever let me run,* says Cushi; come what will, I will venture to deliver the message to David.

**V. 14. ***{Wherefore do I take my flesh in my teeth, and put my life in my hand?}* Having exhorted them to attention, he sets out the greatness of his grief, and maintains his innocency; sometimes turning his speech to them, and sometime to God, as a man perplexed, or conceiving he had wrong from both.

*{Wherefore}* What sin have I committed so great, that I should be thus severely punished? Some think it to be a challenge to his friends, to tell the cause of it, if they could. As our Savior challenges the Pharisees, John 8. 46. Others, for a turning of his speech from them to God, (as Jacob does, Gen. 49. 18.) desiring God to show him, why he was so sorely afflicted, and in his distemper, conceiving no just cause could be given.

*{do I}* If God afflict none but ungodly men, as you say, why am I then so heavily afflicted, that fear God?

*{take my flesh in my teeth}* Why have I such great pains put upon me, which are enough to make me not only tear my garments, but also my very flesh. Not that Job did so, or was thus cruel against himself, but alluding to the impatient gestures of others, to set out the greatness of his own miseries. See Joel 2. 13. Rev. 16. 10. Chap. 18. 4. Or, he intimates, that his flesh and skin was so worn, that he might easily tear them by bits; or so full of holes, by reason of sores, as if he had bitten himself all over, Chap. 19. 20. The first interpretation is best, and most agreeable to Scripture language. For *Take,* see on Chap. 21. 3. on *Suffer.* For *Flesh,* see on Chap. 14. 22. For *Teeth,* see on Chap. 16. 9.

*{and put my life in my hand}* Why am I so sorely afflicted, that I am every moment in danger of death, as if I had my life in my hand, ready to be taken away from me? A thing in the hand, may easily be let fall, or taken away: So Job's extreme pains, might make him think himself near death. So we say of a man in a great fear, His heart is at his mouth. For the phrase, see Judg. 12. 3. 1 Sam. 19. 5. & 28. 21. Psal. 119. 109. For *Life,* see on Chap. 14. 22. on *Soul.* And on Chap. 12. 10. For *Hand,* see on Chap. 36. 32. on *Clouds.*

**V. 15. ***{Though he slay me, yet will I trust in him: but I will maintain mine own ways before him}* As if he had said, You go about to drive me to despair, because of the great plagues that God has laid upon me: but be it known to you, if God should double his stripes, and lay greater afflictions upon me, yea, if he should take away my life, yet would I, in the midst of death, expect a better life from him, how hardly soever he dealt with me here. And yet on the other side, I would maintain my sincerity, and that I have not deserved more than other men, to be thus extraordinarily plagued. Here you see plainly in Job, an extraordinary combat, between the spirit and the flesh.

*{Though he slay me}* He speaks of God, mentioned, verse 8. and spoken of in the verses following. Though he bring judgment after judgment upon me, till he make an end of me, yet my confidence shall not fail. This argues a strong faith in Job, notwithstanding his impatient slips in his heavy trials. See the like, Psal. 23. 4. Prov. 14. 32. For *Though,* see on Chap. 12. 14. on *Behold.*

*{yet will I trust in him}* I will not cast off my confidence in God, while I have one minute to live. An heroical resolution in a man in Job's condition. Others read it thus, *Though he slay me, and I should have no hope.* To wit, of living any longer; yet I would stand to my innocency to the death. But this takes away the demonstration of Job's faith.

*{but I will maintain}* Or, *Plead for;* as verse 3, 6. Heb. *prove,* or, *argue.* For *Maintain,* see on Chap. 15. 3. on *Reason.*

*{mine own ways}* The course of my life, consisting of many actions, as so many steps, Chap. 8. 19. Psal. 1. 1. & 119. 1. I would prove my life to be upright without hypocrisy. For *Way,* see on Chap. 17. 9.

*{before him}* Heb. *to his face,* This phrase argues confidence.

**V. 16. ***{He also shall be my salvation: for an hypocrite shall not come before him}* In this verse Job gives a double reason of his forementioned confidence in God. The one, from God's readiness to help such as are afflicted. The other, from the sincerity of his own heart, and freeness from hypocrisy.

*{He also shall be my salvation}* Heb. *He himself also shall be to me for salvation.* God himself, spoken of before, verse 15. Salvation may be taken for temporal deliverance from his pains, and the unjust censures of his friends. But it is rather to be interpreted of eternal life, which would befall him after death, as verse 15. and which no hypocrite shall enjoy; as follows in this verse. Others read it, *That also shall be to me for salvation.* This trusting in God, in death, shall bring me to eternal life. Or, This maintaining my cause, shall further my deliverance out of these straits. For *Also,* see on Chap. 16. 4. The word הוא sometimes signifies the same, as Psal. 102. 27.

*{for}* This shows my sincere innocency, that I dare thus plead my cause before God himself, which no hypocrite dares do, though he can do it before men. Thus he answers to Eliphaz, and Bildad charging him with hypocrisy, Chap. 4. 6. & 8. 13.

*{an hypocrite}* One that carries out his cause with a bold face before men, but knows that he is guilty, and that God knows it also, and therefore dares not refer the matter to God, who cannot be deceived. See on Chap. 8. 13.

*{shall not come before him}* In heaven, as I hope to do: or, to plead his cause with confidence, as I am ready to do, before God's judgment seat. For *Come,* see on Chap. 15. 21.

**V. 17. ***{Hear diligently my speech, and my declaration with your ears}* He had called for audience once before, in verse 6. and dissuaded them from interrupting him, verse 13. It seems the things he spoke, were such as his friends had no mind to hear, and did seem impatient and willing to interrupt him, and yet were things needful to be heard both for his defense, and their informations; and therefore now again he calls for audience, and that more earnestly than before, *Hear diligently,* &c. It may be conceived that they thought, that a man in such misery as he was, could not have so much confidence in God as he had expressed, verse 15, 16. and therefore he calls on them, to mind his words.

*{Hear diligently}* Heb. *Hearing hear.* The doubling the word increases the signification: see on verse 5. The Hebrew word שמע has many senses in Scripture. 1. It signifies to hear, as in hearing a report, Gen. 42. 2. Obad. verse 1. 2. To understand, as Gen. 42. 23. for they that understand not what is spoken, get no more good by it, than they that do not hear it at all, 1 Cor. 14. 8, 9, 10, 11. 3. To mark what is said, or to attend to it, Gen. 29. 33. 4. To grant what is asked, Gen. 17. 20. 5. To believe a thing reported to be true, Exod. 6. 9. 6. To obey, Gen. 26. 5. Here it is taken in the third sense for marking; for they did hear before, but did not mark Job's words, that they might understand the meaning of them.

*{my speech}* My continued oration.

*{and my declaration}* Either he means the declaration of his sorrows to move his friends to pity him, or else of the arguments he had to maintain his innocency, to keep them from condemning him.
