*{with your ears}* Heb. *in your ears.* That is, which I now purpose to speak in your hearing. But it is better translated, *with your ears;* for the ears are the sense of hearing, and he would have them to employ their ears altogether in hearing him at this time. So the particle ב is used, Exod. 21. 18. Job 7. 14. & 9. 30.

**V. 18. ***{Behold now I have ordered my cause, I know that I shall be justified}* Here Job prepares for his trial before God, which he begins, verse 23. and holds on to the end of the fourteenth chapter. In this preparation, he first sets out his care to set his plea in order, and his confidence of prevailing, in this verse. 2. His desire of an enemy that would plead against him, and the necessity of his pleading, verse 19. 3. His desire of removal of hindrances, that so he might have a fair trial, verse 20, 21, 22. 4. An offer of the choice to God, whether he would reply or answer: or as the lawyers speak, whether he would be plaintiff, or defendant; so confident was he of prevailing. In all this preparation, and likewise in the whole plea, to the end of the fourteenth chapter, Job in his impatience, pleads against God's harsh dealing with him; for all his speech is directed to God, and nothing charged upon his friends.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{now}* At present I have done it, and am ready to plead. Or, *I pray you;* do you I beseech you take notice of my readiness.

*{I have ordered}* I have set all my arguments in order; as soldiers are set in battle array, or produce them orderly one after another: See on Chap. 6. 4.

*{my cause}* Heb. *my judgement,* see on Chap. 9. 32.

*{I know}* I am sure beforehand, that I shall clear myself.

*{that I shall be justified}* This word in Scripture sometimes signifies making just, as Dan. 12. 3. where it is translated turning many to righteousness: sometimes it signifies, declaring one to be just, and that either in a particular cause; as Psal. 51. 4. Deut. 25. 1. or declaring the person to be just, and that either comparatively, in respect of others more wicked, as Ezek. 16. 51. or absolutely, that he is truly just, Isai. 53. 11. Job 25. 4. Here it may be taken either, that he should prove himself (if he might be heard at large) just in this cause, or a man truly fearing God.

**V. 19. ***{Who is he that will plead with me? for now if I hold my tongue, I shall give up the ghost}* He speaks as one confident; who dare accuse me? I defy all men that shall charge me with wickedness. And withal, it declares an earnest desire of a present adversary to plead with him, so earnest was he to be cleared.

*{who is he}* I would there were any such, and that he would appear presently to make good his charge, if he can.

*{that will plead with me?}* See on verse 3, 6.

*{for}* This word ushers in a reason, from the impatience of delay.

*{now}* If it be not dispatched presently, I can stay no longer.

*{If I hold my tongue}* See on verse 5.

*{I shall give up the ghost}* I shall die presently. See on Chap. 3. 11. & 10. 18. & 11. 20. Either Job intends to show the greatness of his passion, that could no longer endure such an aspersion to lie upon him, but had provided such store of matter in his own defense, that his passion would make an end of him, if he could not vent it, as Chap. 7. 11. Or else to set out the greatness of his pains which would quickly make an end of him, if they continued, and then he should not be cleared before he died. It would be too late then to plead his cause. See the like, Chap. 16. 21, 22. The first sense is the best.

**V. 20. ***{Only do not two things unto me; then will I not hide myself from thee}* In this verse he puts up a general petition to God for a fair trial, and to that end desires that two things might be removed, before he begins to plead for himself, which were likely to hinder him much in his plea, and which he names in the next verse.

*{Only do not two things unto me}* I have no more to desire but these two things. If they may be granted to me, I will go on in my plea; otherwise I must make a stop, as being out of hope of any fair trial. And here he begins to turn his speech to God, to make way for his ensuing plea.

*{then will I not hide myself from thee}* Heb. *from thy face.* Two things make men hide themselves when they should plead their cause: The one is, their own guilt; and the other, their enemies' power. The first, Job speaks as if it did not move him at all. All that he feared was the last, lest being in this miserable condition, he should be overpowered by God, and so daunted, that he could not follow his cause with any comfort. When he says, he would not hide himself out of God's sight, he means, that he would come boldly into God's presence to plead his cause, whensoever God should call for him. So he speaks, Chap. 9. 35. It is a Meiosis, as Chap. 12. 3. See there.

**V. 21. ***{Withdraw thine hand far from me: and let not thy dread make me afraid}* Here Job specifies the two things that lay so sore upon his spirit, that he could not freely plead his cause; To wit, God's afflicting hand, and majestical apparition. And he prays that these might be removed, that he might go on freely in pleading his cause. The same things he desired before, Chap. 9. 34. See there for the sense of this place. We shall here note the sense of such words, as differ. It is also worth the observing, That Elihu, Chap. 33. 6, 7. shows that he understood Job thus, and therefore offered to plead for God with him, having neither power to afflict him, nor majesty to affright him.

*{Withdraw far}* Heb. *Remove far off.* In Chap. 9. 34. it is but *taking away:* here it is, *putting far away.* And it shows, that Job had been so sorely handled, that he should not be free from fears, though his pain should be taken away, unless he were assured, that they were so far removed, that they might not return, till he had quite done pleading his cause. The removal of his pains could not remove his fears, unless they were removed far away, as the sins of God's people are, Psal. 103. 12.

*{thine hand}* The plague which you have laid upon me. A metaphor with a trope of the cause for the effect. It is *his rod,* Chap. 9. 34. for men strike sometimes with their hands, and sometimes with rods, when they make wounds. For the phrase here, the *hand* for the *stroke,* or *plague,* see Exod. 9. 3. Judg. 2. 15. Ruth 1. 13. 1 Sam. 5. 6, 7, 11. Psal. 81. 14. Lam. 3. 8. Acts 13. 11. The rest of the verse was interpreted on Chap. 9. 34. See there.

**V. 22. ***{Then call thou, and I will answer: or let me speak, and answer thou me.}* He now goes on to set out his fearlessness of appearing before God upon the former terms, and his readiness to plead his cause, and preparedness on both sides to object or answer; else would he not have offered the choice to God.

*{Then}* Heb. *And.* But this particle is often translated *then,* as Gen. 28. 21. Chap. 1. 20. & 7. 14. when you have granted me the two former requests, (mentioned verse 21.) then I am ready to plead for myself. I have no other obstacle to hinder me, for I know my cause is just.

*{call thou}* This word is a most fruitful word, having a multitude of significations in Scripture. The word in the original is קרא. It signifies, 1. To cry, Isa. 58. 1. 2. To proclaim, or publish a thing, Isa. 40. 3, 6. 2 King. 23. 16, 17. 3. To exclaim, or cry out, Gen. 39. 15. 4. To read, Josh. 8. 34. 5. To impose a name, Gen. 29. 32, 33, 34, 35. 6. To call one by his name, 1 Sam. 3. 4, 6. 7. To call upon or pray to God, Psal. 50. 15. & 116. 4. 8. To call one to him, Hos. 11. 2. 9. To call men together, Exod. 12. 16. Joel 1. 14. 10. To invite to a feast, Esth. 5. 12. 11. To call into judgment, or call to account, Gen. 3. 9. 1 King. 2. 36. 12. To meet with one, Gen. 46. 29. 13. To happen or come to pass unexpectedly, 2 Sam. 1. 6. & 20. 1. Here it is taken in the eleventh sense, for calling into judgment. As if he had said, Call you me to account, and plead against me.

*{and I will answer}* I will not only answer to my name called, as men do in courts of justice, but also be the defendant, and give a particular answer to all things which you object against me, and maintain, that I have given you no such cause to lay so heavy afflictions upon me, to make me an example of your indignation in the eyes of the world.

*{or let me speak}* Heb. *Or, I will speak.* That is, I will be the plaintiff, and object all my pains and sorrows laid so heavily upon me, to see what answer you will give, why I am thus afflicted more than others.

*{and answer thou me}* Do you give answer to what I shall object. This appears by the beginning of Job's charge in the next verse, to be his meaning. And Mercer, a learned commentarer on the text, lays a great charge on Job in this place, that he was *Omnino semper immodicus, Altogether alwayes immoderate;* to wit, in defending himself, and charging God so highly in this and the next chapter.

**V. 23. ***{How many are mine iniquities and sins? make me to know my transgression, and my sin.}* Because God will not begin, Job will, and so he complains of God's hard dealing with him, and desires to know the cause of it. Do not condemn me unheard. Let me know the cause why I am thus tortured. Unparalleled plagues should proceed from unparalleled sins. And many plagues should come from many sins. Show me therefore what and how many my sins are, that require so many and great punishments. Yet I know none such by myself, neither can my friends convince me of such. Let me know them, or let my plagues be taken away. This is the sum of Job’s plea in this verse.

*{How many}* The word properly intimates inquiry after a great number, as Zech. 7. 3. Chap. 21. 17. Psal. 78. 40. & 119. 84. As if Job had said, My friends and the world judge me guilty of many sins, because of your extraordinary dealing with me. Clear the matter before me and them.

*{are mine iniquities}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{and sins}* See on Chap. 5. 24. & 7. 20. & 10. 6.

*{make me to know}* Show me. See on Chap. 10. 2.

*{my transgression}* See on Chap. 7. 21. It seems by the alteration of the number, that Job aims at some great enormous sin or rebellion. As if he had said, If you cannot accuse me of many great offenses, then show me that one grievous sin, that has provoked you to lay such load upon me.

*{and my sin}* See on chap. 5. 24. & 7. 20. & 10. 6. If not many sins requiring such an exemplary punishment, yet at least produce one.

**V. 24. ***{Wherefore hidest thou thy face, and holdest me for thine enemy?}* Seeing therefore you cannot bring forth any heinous crimes against me, that I am guilty of, for which I should be so severely punished, why do you like an angry man turn your face away from me, and handle me, as if you did hate me? Chap. 7. 20. Thus he sets out the severity of God’s displeasure by degrees; first, in not showing any countenance to him: secondly, in laying heavy load upon him.

*{Wherefore}* Show me the cause of this great alteration. You were wont to shine upon me, Chap. 29. 2, 3. Why do you deal otherwise with me now? what so great cause have I given you of so great a change?

*{hidest thou thy face}* God is said to hide his face from his people, when he regards not their prayer in their miseries; like an angry man, who by turning his face away, shows, that he neither pities, nor purposes to relieve. See Deut. 31. 17. Psal. 10. 1. & 13. 1. & 69. 17. & 102. 2. Isa. 54. 8. Mic. 3. 4. As on the other side, he is said to turn his face to his servants, or shine upon them, when he delivers, or keeps them out of trouble, or blesses them extraordinarily. See Psal. 31. 15, 16. & 67. 1. & 80. 3, 7. & 119. 135. Num. 6. 25, 26.

*{and holdest me}* Heb. *thinkest me*; or, *accountest me*, Gen. 31. 15.

*{for thine enemy}* Wound me, and plague me, as if you did esteem me an enemy, and had a purpose to torment, and to destroy me. See Chap. 16. 9. & 19. 11. & 33. 10. Lam. 2. 4, 5. God did not only withdraw help from him in these his great trials, but also lay greater troubles on him daily.

**V. 25. ***{Wilt thou break a leaf driven to and fro? and wilt thou pursue the dry stubble?}* He sets out his own weakness, compared with God’s greatness, by two similitudes. The one, of the leaf, and the wind; the other of the stubble and the fire. Intimating, that God would get no great glory to himself, by afflicting so weak a creature as he was. No man would account it an honor to a strong champion, to overcome a weak bedrid man.

*{Wilt thou}* Does it become you so to do? Do you think to get any honor by it? Surely you will not. The affirmative question makes the more vehement negation.

*{break}* Or, *terrify*. For the word signifies both: and thence comes a word, which signifies men in great power, Psal. 37. 35. Such as by their greatness terrify and break others.

*{a leaf}* It has this name in Hebrew from ascending, because it comes up out of the tree, and grows upon high trees. A leaf is a slight thing. So was Job in this his weak condition.

*{driven to and fro}* To wit, by the wind, which shakes it to and fro, when it is on the tree, quickly blows it off, and then gives it no resting place.

*{and wilt thou pursue}* Either it may be meant of blowing stubble away by the wind, as Psal. 1. 4. or casting it into the fire.

*{dry stubble}* Stubble is light of itself, but being dry, it is the easier blown away, or the quicker burnt. So was Job as weak as a leaf on a tree, or stubble, in regard of his human nature, and like a leaf blown up and down, or stubble cast into the fire by reason of his afflictions. See the like similitudes, Isa. 42. 3. Two things are intended by these two similitudes: First, that God did use his great power against a weak man, which was no honor to him, as Job conceived. Secondly, that he did add affliction to his affliction, which he thought to be some dishonor to him, that having blown him off from the tree of his prosperity, like a leaf, and dried him like stubble with afflictions; yet he gave him no rest, but blew him from one trouble to another, and threatens him with death, which might now as easily be inflicted on him, as a leaf of a tree be blown away, or as any stubble be cast into the fire and burned.

**V. 26. ***{For thou writest bitter things against me, and makest me to possess the iniquities of my youth}* In this verse, and the rest to the end of the chapter, Job goes on in a judiciary way, comparing himself to the party accused, and God to the judge, who ransacks all his life, causes all his faults to be written down, and severe punishments for them, pronounces him guilty, passes a heavy sentence upon him, and sees it executed without mercy.

*{For}* He sets down the reason why he thought that God did intend to ruin him, to wit, because he did proceed so sharply against him, as if he meant to make him answer for all the sins of his life at once.

*{thou writest}* Some take it to be understood of the book of God’s decrees: but that is from eternity, and Job speaks here of God’s present proceedings against him, taking notice of all things objected against him, and determining now to execute a sharp sentence upon him, as sure as judges do their sentences written down and pronounced against malefactors. This phrase of writing, shows also the unalterableness of the sentence, as Job. 19. 22. Psal. 149. 9. Dan. 6. 8.

*{bitter things}* Or, *bitternesses*. My good deeds are all forgotten, but my sins are all written down, that you might produce them to condemn me; as follows in this verse. Sin may be called bitterness, as Deut. 29. 18, 19. Isa. 5. 20. Jer. 2. 19. Act. 8. 23. Rom. 3. 14. Heb. 12. 15. Jam. 3. 11, 14. because it is displeasing to God, as bitter things are to us, and brings bitter and displeasing things upon us. But here it is rather to be understood of punishments and afflictions; for his sins are mentioned distinctly in the end of the verse. And afflictions may be called bitter things, because they make our lives bitter, Exod. 1. 14. For as bitter things are most displeasing to the taste, so are afflictions both to soul and body, Ruth 1. 20. Especially sharp afflictions may be meant here, such as bring death with them; for bitterness and death, often go together in Scripture, 1 King. 4. 39, 40. Bitter herbs in the pot threaten death, 1 Sam. 15. 32.

*{against me}* To be executed upon me.

*{and makest me to possess}* Or *to inherit*. For possessions come most commonly by inheritance from parents. You lay on me the punishments of my former sins, as if they came to me by inheritance: or you make them stick as fast to me, as a possession does to the purchaser. You bring them to my remembrance afresh, and lay them anew to my charge, having concealed them for a long time in sparing me hitherto, but now you lay such heavy load on me, as if you had watched a time to be revenged on me for all together.

*{the iniquities}* For this word, See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{of my youth}* It is not enough for you to punish me for the sins of my age, but you proceed against me also, for my youthful sins long since out of ignorance committed, and not since renewed, and which I thought by your kind dealing with me, had been long ago forgiven, and forgotten. So sharply do you deal with me, and take all advantages against me. The word in the original is *youths*, in the plural number; because it contains not only manly years, but also childhood, boyish time, youthful time, and all a man’s days from his birth, till he come to a manly age. The word comes from נער, to shake out, Neh. 5. 13. for infants are as it were shaken out of the mother’s womb into the world. And young men are set at liberty from masters, and tutors, and left to themselves, and then for want of experience they do many things amiss: and it may be Job had done so: See the like, Chap. 20. 11. Psal. 25. 7.

**V. 27. ***{Thou puttest my feet also in the stocks, and lookest narrowly into all my paths; thou settest a print upon the heels of my feet}* Here begins the execution, which used to follow the sentence given, and it is a very strict one.

*{thou puttest my feet also in the stocks}* An allusion to an usual kind of punishment, of putting men in the stocks; either to shame them for some evil done, or to keep them safe for greater punishment: the latter seems to be meant here. As if he had said, You kept me in on every side with pain, as a prisoner in the stocks; and are resolved to keep me so, till you have made an end of me. As appears, verse 28. See Chap. 33. 11. & 16. 8. Jer. 20. 2, 3. & 29. 26. Others read, *In the chalk*. You uncover all my sins, as men do their footsteps, that walk in chalky, or clay ground, Chap. 14. 16. But the former interpretation suits best with the similitude of a prisoner.

*{and lookest narrowly}* Heb. *observest*. As men use to watch those that are put into the stocks, that they may not escape; so do you take away from me all means of escaping these troubles. They will certainly consume me. Others read; *It keeps in*. The stocks keep in the prisoner, so that he has no way of escaping; and so do these afflictions keep me fast. For the word, See on Chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14.

*{into all my paths}* Some understand it, of observing all his misdemeanors, both before his troubles, and under them: That God inquired into them strictly, to take advantage against him, and punish him the more. But it is rather meant of all ways of escaping. If I should think of any way of getting out of these troubles, you find it out always, and prevent me, and so keep me in still, in the prison of these afflictions. For the word, See on Chap. 6. 18, 19.

*{Thou settest a print}* Or, *Engravest thy self*. You not only do as it were set a watch on me, or stand aloof off to observe me, but also follow me step by step; as hunters follow wild beasts in the snow, by their footsteps. Some take it, of observing all his sinful actions. But it is rather meant of ways of escaping. If I tread but one step towards delivery, you follow me close to prevent me, as if you did tread in every step after me, and would not be one step behind me, so that I cannot possibly escape. Others read, *It engraveth itself*; That is, as a narrow pair of stocks leaves the print of the wood upon the prisoner’s heel, so my afflictions leave sad marks upon me. But the former is better.

*{upon the heels of my feet}* Heb. *upon the roots of my feet*. Which may better be understood of the soles of the feet, than of the heels, because they are the lowest part of the feet, as the roots are of the trees. And by a figure may be meant the footsteps, which the heels make. As in the last note before.

**V. 28. ***{And he as a rotten thing consumeth, and as a garment that is moth-eaten}* Here is the conclusion of this similitude, wherein Job has proceeded by an elegant and passionate gradation, to set forth God’s harsh dealing with him, as he conceived. First you deny favor to me, hiding your face from me. Then you show enmity against me, ver. 24. And that having no respect to my frail condition, but showing your great power in persecuting me without mercy, ver. 25. And like an angry judge resolved to condemn, you ransack all my life, and pass a heavy sentence upon me for sins long since committed, verse 26. Not so only, but you clap me fast in prison, tied up in the chains of afflictions, like a malefactor; and are become the jailer yourself, to observe every step I make, and to follow me close, that I may be sure not to escape, till your anger have seized fully upon me, verse 27. And in this sad condition do I still lie, consuming and moldering away under your heavy indignation, verse 28.

*{And he}* Or, *It*. Some understand it of the leaf or stubble spoken of before, verse 25. Some take it to be meant of the heel, or foot, hurt by the narrowness of the hole in the stocks, verse 27. and so putrefying. Others read it, *This*. That is, This body of mine, as Chap. 19. 26. Job pointing to it, as it is like he did there, and Paul, 1 Cor. 15. 53, 54. They which read it, *He*, understand it of a man in such a wretched condition, as is before described, yet with relation to Job himself, as Chap. 7. 1. & 14. 1.

*{as a rotten thing}* Heb. *as rottenness*. The abstract for the concrete, to increase the signification. As the most rotten thing that is. Others read it, *As with rottenness*. For the rot consumes the strongest cattle, if once it seize upon them.

*{consumeth}* Heb. *waxeth old*. So the same word is translated, Psal. 102. 26. Now because old age does consume strength and beauty, and bring men to the grave, it signifies also to consume. But rottenness makes men old before their time, and sorrow and affliction bring men into a consumption, and kill them before old age come.

*{and as a garment}* As a moth-eaten garment grows old, Psal. 102. 26. and quickly wears quite away. For the word בגד, *a garment*. See Chap. 6. 15.

*{that is moth-eaten}* That is food for moths, and devoured by them, as meat is by men. Hereby a secret and insensible consumption, by great pains and sorrows is intended; as Psal. 39. 11. See on Chap. 4. 19.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1. ***{Man that is born of a woman is of few days, and full of troubles.}* Job takes occasion from the conclusion of the former chapter, to speak of man’s misery, with a special relation to himself; and sets it out by the original of it, in his birth, the progress in a miserable and short life, the issue of it in his death.

*{Man}* Adam. An earthly creature, born of one that was made of the earth at first. So much the word originally imports, and God objects to him after his fall, to humble him, Gen. 2. 7. & 3. 19. It was the proper name of the first man, Gen. 2. 20. but afterwards affixed to the nature, and communicated to all his posterity. So was not Eve, the first woman’s name communicated to women, because she herself, and so by the consequence of all that came from her, came out of Adam.

*{that is born of a woman}* Of a weak woman full of sorrows in breeding and bearing, and bringing forth an issue like herself, Gen. 3. 19. Thus man is often described in Scripture, Chap. 15. 14. & 25. 4. Matth. 11. 11. Luke 7. 28. And he is said to be born of a woman, rather than of a man, because the woman’s pains are great in bringing him forth, and he comes immediately out of her womb into the world. The word אשה, *woman*, is taken from איש, *a man*, because the first woman had both her nature and her name from the man, Gen. 2. 23.

*{is of few days}* Heb. *short of dayes*. That is, of a short life, Gen. 47. 9. Psal. 90. 5, 6, 9. & 102. 11. & 103. 15. & 144. 4. Jam. 4. 14. So *length of dayes*, imports a long life, Exod. 20. 12. Prov. 3. 2, 16. His days are short in themselves, shorter in regard of his troubles, which afford little comfort to him, and small time worthy the name of life, and threaten death to him daily.

*{and full of trouble}* Heb. *satisfied with commotion*. For the word *full*, see on Chap. 7. 4. & 10. 10, 15. For the other word, *trouble*, see the force of it, on Chap. 3. 17. & 9. 6. & 12. 6. It imports, that troubles, as they move the mind with passion, so they cause men oftentimes to remove from place to place for safety, Psal. 105. 13. Heb. 11. 37, 38. The sum of this verse is, that man leads but a short and miserable life here, especially such men as are in Job’s condition. See Chap. 7. 1, &c. Eccles. 2. 23.

**V. 2. ***{He cometh forth like a flower, and is cut down; he flyeth also as a shadow, and continueth not.}* Here he sets out the shortness of man’s life, by two elegant similitudes frequently used in Scripture. The one taken from a flower, which is no sooner seen grown, but it withers: the other from a shadow, that never is of any long continuance. See Chap. 8. 9. Psal. 102. 11. & 103. 15. & 144. 4. 1 Chr. 29. 15. Isa. 40. 6. 1 Pet. 1. 24.

*{He cometh forth}* Man comes forth out of his mother’s womb, as a flower out of the bowels of the earth, Chap. 3. 11. He comes forth into the light to be seen of men, that lay hid before, as a flower in the stalk.

*{like a flower}* He makes a fair show in the world, when he is born, as a flower does in the garden, when it is shot forth.

*{and}* That is, as soon as he is born, he tends towards death, as a flower to decaying. See on Chap. 3. 11.

*{is cut down}* Heb. *cut round*; or *circumcised*. It is gathered or cropped with hand, or the leaves are beaten off quite round with the wind, or wither and fall away of themselves in a little time. So man is subject to many dangers, that every day threaten his life, and if they dispatch him not, will soon die of himself.

*{he flyeth also}* The word imports haste; as Jon. 1. 3. Nah. 3. 17. Isa. 48. 20. Man’s days go away as fast as one that flies for his life. See on Chap. 9. 25.

*{as a shadow}* Most swiftly; for nothing is more fleeting than a shadow. See on Chap. 8. 9.

*{and continueth not}* Heb. *standeth not*. To wit, in the same state and condition. So the word is used, Eccl. 1. 4. The shadow is suddenly withdrawn from the sight of man, either by some clouds interposing in the day, or by the night approaching and swallowing it up in darkness. So man’s life is either taken away by some unexpected perils, or ends when nature has spent her strength. It spends apace, and cannot tarry long.

**V. 3. ***{And dost thou open thine eyes upon such an one, and bringest me into judgment with thee?}* Does it become so great a God to oppose himself to so vile a creature, and to observe all his courses to find matter against him, as if it were a great glory to you, to trample under feet such a worm as I am? See much to the same purpose on Chapter 7. 17, 18.

*{And dost thou}* Spoken with a kind of indignation and admiration, and therefore expressed by way of interrogation, to show the vehemency of Job’s disturbed passion. Seeing man is so short-lived and miserable, will you look upon so vile an object?

*{open thine eyes}* Some understand it barely of God’s providence taking notice of so vile a creature; as opening the mouth or lips, is no more but speaking, opening the ears, hearing: but it is rather to be interpreted of God’s opening his eyes in anger, to observe all Job’s faults, and to spy out some exquisite way of punishment. See on Chap. 7. 8. Zech. 12. 4. To look on such a wretch so much as to observe his faults, and punish him thus for them, is beneath your care. As to open the hand, is to be bountiful; so to open the eye, is to observe men’s faults strictly with a purpose to punish them severely. As on the contrary, he that intends not to punish a fault, shuts his eyes, that he may not see it.

*{upon such an one}* Heb. *upon this man*. Upon so short-lived miserable a creature.

*{and bringest me}* Heb. *and makest me to come*. Now he shows, that though he spoke of man in general, yet he had relation to his own condition, more miserable at present than other men.

*{into judgment}* Deal with him *summo jure*, and most rigidly, Psal. 143. 2. He had desired before to plead with God, Chap. 13. 3. but with condition of removing his plague, and God’s terror from him, Chap. 13. 20, 21. which not being done, he is afraid to come into judgment. For the word *judgement*, see on Chap. 8. 3. & 9. 22.

*{with thee}* Me so frail and miserable man, with you so great and powerful a God. Here it appears that Job did not intend to plead with his friends before God, but with God himself, as one that had dealt too harshly with him. See more to this purpose on Chap. 13. 3. The text may be read thus, without an interrogation; *Yet thou dost open thine eyes upon such an one, and bring me into judgement with thee.* Though I be thus short-lived and miserable, and should rather be pitied than afflicted, yet you deal extremely with me. So this particle ו is used, Psal. 73. 16. Or thus, *And dost open thine eyes for this, that thou shouldest bring me into judgement with thee?* As if this were the main thing that God looked at in his providence, to afflict poor Job. See the like phrase, Exod. 13. 8. Amos 1. 3, 6. And for ו *that*, see Numb. 8. 19. And for the future translated *should*, see Gen. 34. 31.

**V. 4. ***{Who can bring a clean thing out of an unclean? not one}* Why will you punish man so severely for sin, who is naturally corrupt, and cannot avoid sin, being born in a sinful condition? And why am I singled out from among others, to endure such exquisite torments, who was no better by birth than they, nor am no worse in life? To avoid such a rigid judgment, he objects the common infirmity of nature. I should rather be pitied, than thus sharply punished, because I am naturally inclined to sin.

*{Who can bring}* Heb. *who will give*. For the future translated *can*, see on Chap. 8. 11. The word נתן has many significations in Scripture. First, for delivering a thing into one’s hand, not to possess, but to guide, as Gen. 39. 4, 22. 2. To put or place, as Gen. 1. 17. 3. To suffer, as Exod. 12. 23. 4. To make, as Ezek. 6. 14. 5. To set over, as Gen. 41. 41. 6. To teach, as Prov. 9. 9. 7. To account, as Gen. 42. 30, 8. To signify wishing, as Numb. 11. 29. 9. To bring forth one thing out of another, as Psal. 1. 3. and so here.

*{a clean thing}* The word, literally, signifies bodily cleanness, freedom from filth, scabs, leprosy; 2 King. 5. 10. Figuratively, yet more frequently, it imports a freedom from sin, Psal. 19. 9. & 51. 7. Here it may be meant, as a common proverb, As no man can bring a clean stream out of a dirty puddle, so can no man bring a man free from sin into the world, so long as his parents are tainted with original sin. This doctrine is held out in many Scriptures, Gen. 5. 3. Psal. 51. 5. Joh. 3. 6. Rom. 5. 12. Eph. 2. 3.

*{out of an unclean}* It literally signifies an unclean thing, or place, soiled with rubbish, or dead carcasses, as 2 King. 23. 10. and figuratively, though more commonly one that is polluted with sin, which defiles the soul, Rev. 21. 27. See Gen. 34. 5. Lev. 18. 23, 24. So it is just contrary to the former word. And intimates, here, our original corruption, received from our parents. Man’s seed is not unclean in itself, as it was created, but became so by the fall, and so we are unclean, being born of it.

*{not one}* No man on the face of the earth can do this. It is too great a work for any creature to undertake. See the like phrase, Psal. 14. 3. & 53. 3. Others read it, *Canst not thou alone?* It may be the reason that moved them, might be, because Christ was born clean of a sinful mother. But the former translation will clear that well enough, No earthly father could have begotten him in such a condition. He was so conceived in an extraordinary way, by the power of the Holy Ghost, Luke 1. 34, 35. It is not likely that Job, in this passionate fit, looked so far, but only gave an answer to his own question, signifying, that no mere man could bring forth a clean child, out of unclean seed.

**V. 5. ***{Seeing his days are determined, the number of his months are with thee, thou hast appointed his bounds, that he cannot pass}* Seeing you have set him a short time in the world, and when that time comes, he can live no longer, nor ever return into this world any more, it is very fitting he should enjoy some time of refreshing here, before he be taken out of the world.

*{Seeing}* Heb. *If*. But it does not signify here an uncertain condition, as often elsewhere, but a certain position of a known truth, and a conclusion deduced from it, verse 6. So it is translated *sith*. Ezek. 35. 6. and the sense requires it so to be understood, 2 King. 20. 19. Esth. 6. 13.

*{his days are determined}* See on Chap. 7. 1. & verse 1. of this chapter; *his dayes*, verse 1, &c. Determined; to have a certain end, or to be cut off; as this word imports, Joel 3. 14.

*{the number}* Rather, *and the number*. So this particle is added, Chap. 38. 40. The sum of them is determined.

*{of his months}* Of the months he is to live. It comes from a word that signifies to renew, and it is used for the new moon, at which the Jews began their months, and by a figure of the part for the whole month.

*{is with thee}* You only know it, or rather, you have determined it. And so it agrees well with the beginning, and end of the verse.

*{thou hast appointed}* Heb. *thou hast made*. It may be read, and you have appointed; as was noted before, for all these things hang together, and the inference follows in the next verse.

*{his bounds}* Heb. *his statutes*. The bounds of his life, and those far shorter, than men lived before the flood. You have set the beginning and end of his life, when it shall be. These are the two bounds of man’s abiding in this world, Chap. 7. 1.

*{that he cannot pass}* God’s decrees, concerning the time of man’s life, are so certain, that he cannot go beyond them, but must certainly die, when his time comes. As the sea, for all her raging, cannot pass her bounds, Chap. 38. 8, &c. Prov. 8. 29. Psal. 104. 9. The word of *passing*, agrees well with a bound, or statute; Heb. 9. 27. Act. 17. 26. For ו *that*, see Chap. 23. 13. Passing, here, is put for going beyond, as Prov. 8. 29. Psal. 104. 9. There is no passing over the bounds, which God has set. Kings and emperors must die, when their appointed time comes.

**V. 6. ***{Turn from him that he may rest, till he shall accomplish, as an hireling, his day}* Seeing his time of life here is by you determined, and is not long neither, and will bring sorrow enough with it, add not more, by punishing him so sharply for sin, as you do now. It is a small thing to let him live without pains. Old age will come shortly, and make him desire death.

*{Turn from him}* Heb. *Look away from him*. For the word, see on Chap. 7. 19. For the sense, see on Chap. 7. 8. and on the 3. verse of this chapter. Look not so angrily on me to afflict me thus heavily, but turn your angry countenance some other way. He speaks to God, as verse 5.

*{that he may rest}* Or, *cease*, to wit, from his afflictions. Or *that it may cease*, to wit, his plague and heavy affliction. See on Chap. 7. 16. & 13. 20. For ו *that*, see on ver. 3. Or, *and he will cease*. You need not trouble yourself, thus to afflict him, he will die shortly, and cease from living any longer here. So the word is used, Deut. 15. 11. 1 Sam. 2. 5. Judg. 5. 6.

*{till he shall accomplish}* The word signifies, properly, to acquiesce and rest in a thing, or desire it, and the meaning may be, till he be weary of his life, as a hireling of his work, and be willing to die. Yet because the hireling desires not the day, wherein he labors, but to have it ended, that he may rest at night, it may well be meant, till he have accomplished his time here, and growing old, be weary of his life, and willing to die. So the word is by some translated, *accomplishing Sabbaths*, Lev. 26. 34. 2 Chron. 36. 21.

*{as an hireling, his day}* See on Chap. 7. 1, 2.

**V. 7. ***{For there is hope of a tree, if it be cut down, that it will sprout again, and that the tender branch thereof will not cease}* Here Job begins to press God to spare him by a new argument, not taken, as the former, from the shortness of his life, and determined time of his death, but from the impossibility of returning hither, after his death, to enjoy any comfort here. See the like Chap. 7. 6, 7, 8. & 16. 22. Psal. 39. 12, 13. And this argument he illustrates, first, by a similitude, showing man’s condition to be worse than a tree, which may grow again in the same place, though it be cut down, but man once dead, appears no more in this world. Secondly, by a similitude of waters, drying up, verse 11.

*{For}* A reason of the petition, verse 6. Either I shall have comfort in this world, before I die, or never here: therefore grant me rest now.

*{there is hope}* The word signifies somewhat remaining, that may make men expect a renovation. See on Chap. 5. 16. & 7. 6. & 11. 18.

*{of a tree}* Men have some hope, when they cut down a tree by the ground, that it will grow again. Or, *to a tree*. Or, *a tree hath hope*. A metaphor taken from a man, to make the sentence the more emphatical. See the like, Judg. 9. 8, &c. where speech is attributed to trees, as hope is here. See Job 31. 38. For the word עץ, it signifies, 1. wood, Gen. 22. 6. 2. a tree, Gen. 2. 9. 3. a thing made of wood, Ezek. 37. 17. 4. a wooden idol, Jer. 2. 27. 5. a gallows, a thing to hang men on, Gen. 40. 19. 6. a vessel of wood, Exod. 7. 19. 7. a staff of a spear, 1 Sam. 17. 7. Here it is taken in the second sense, for a tree, as often elsewhere.

*{If it be cut down}* Or, *though it be cut down*. So אם, is translated, verse 8. though it be cut even by the ground.

*{that it will sprout again}* Heb. *that it will change*; to wit, its condition, and being cut off will put forth again. Or, *it will change, or renew its strength*, Isai. 40. 31. Thus the word is translated *changing*, verse 14. of this chapter.

*{and that the tender branch thereof}* See on Chap. 8. 16.

*{will not cease}* See on verse 6.

**V. 8. ***{Though the root thereof wax old in the earth, and the stock thereof die in the ground}* Some conceive, that this verse sets out another way of destroying trees, to wit, by old age, though they be not cut down. But it is likely, that he goes on in the same similitude, taken from the same tree, and aggravates the matter by other circumstances. Suppose this tree being cut off by the ground, the root of it left in the earth be old, and so the more unlikely to shoot forth again, and the trunk of it cut off, lying upon the ground by it, give men occasion to think, it will never put out more branches, yet water will revive it.

*{Though}* See on verse 7.

*{the root thereof}* So much of it as grows within the ground, 2 King. 19. 30. It literally signifies the root of an herb or tree, and metaphorically, such as remain of a nation, to give hope of increasing it again, after a great destruction: as in the place cited. See on Chap. 5. 3.

*{wax old}* He does not say, *die*, for then there were no hope of it, but *wax old*, and so be like to decay utterly. Trees have a growing time like men, and a decaying one also, which may well be compared to old age.

*{in the earth}* See on Chap. 9. 24. From the Hebrew word *Erets*, comes our English word *Earth*.

*{and the stock thereof}* It is opposed to the root, and signifies the body of the tree above the ground, which divides itself into branches, Isai. 11. 1. & 40. 24. The stock that was cut off, ver. 7.

*{die}* As it must needs do, being cut off from the root. Trees are said to be dead, when all the moisture is clean gone out of them. So Psal. 78. 47. He destroyed their vines: Heb. *He slew them*.

*{in the ground}* Heb. *in the dust*. It being cut off, and lying in the dust, upon the top of the ground, dies; yet the moistened earth, can put forth other branches out of the root remaining, as verse 9. The word עפר signifies; 1. the earth itself; as it is translated, Chap. 8. 10. 2. the dust that lies upon the face of the earth, 2 Chron. 1. 9. Psal. 18. 42. 3. a mean rank, or condition of men, despised like the dust that is trod under men’s feet, Psal. 113. 7. 4. an innumerable multitude, Psal. 78. 27. 5. a dead man in the grave, Psal. 30. 9. 6. it sets out a low submission, as if the head were bowed down to the dust, Psal. 44. 25. 7. a dejection into a very miserable condition, Isai. 47. 1. 8. a sign of sorrow, Job 2. 12. Here it is taken in the second sense, as was before expounded.

**V. 9. ***{Yet through the scent of water it will bud, and bring forth boughs like a plant.}* Though the stock be dead, and cut away, and the root little better, yet water will revive it.

*{Yet}* Or, *That*. There is hope, that notwithstanding the cutting off, and dying of the stock, and the old age of the root, it will put out fresh boughs, and flourish again, as it did before. So this verse is knit to the seventh verse.

*{through the scent}* Heb. *from the smell*: An usual thing in Scripture, to attribute sense to senseless things; as smelling to the fire, Judg. 16. 9. for so it is in the original there, *when it smelleth the fire*. As man being refreshed with the smell of sweet flowers, is cheered in his spirits, so this decaying tree puts out fresh branches after moisture received, as if it had felt some inward comfort.

*{of water}* The Hebrew word מים has many significations, 1. It signifies water, as Gen. 1. 9. 2. The clouds, the receptacles of rain waters, Psal. 104. 3. 3. It is put for the rain water that comes out of the clouds, Psal. 77. 17. 4. For any kind of drink, as bread is put for any kind of food, Hos. 2. 5. 5. For a multitude, as there are many drops in the water, Psal. 79. 3. 6. For troubles and afflictions, Isai. 43. 2. 7. For the seed of which men are conceived, Isai. 48. 1. 8. For tears; Jer. 9. 1. Lam. 1. 16. Here it is taken in the first sense, when this root of the tree, left in the ground, is watered, either by the moisture of the earth, or by the overflowing of rivers, or by the labor of men, or by the dew or rain from heaven, it will sprout out afresh. See on Chap. 5. 10. & 11. 16. & 12. 15.

*{it will bud}* It will send out buds first, and branches afterwards.

*{and bring forth}* Heb. *make*. The word עשה signifies, 1. To work, make, or do a thing, Gen. 1. 31. 2. To bring forth corn, or fruit, or buds, or branches, or the like, Gen. 41. 47. 3. To dress or prepare, Ezek. 46. 2. 4. To keep, or observe, Josh. 5. 10. 5. To get, or procure, Gen. 31. 1. Deut. 8. 17. Here it is taken in the second sense.

*{boughs}* The word קציר signifies, 1. The time of harvest, when the corn being ripe is cut down, Isa. 6. 3. 2. It is put for the corn, then to be cut down, Joel 3. 13. 3. For war, wherein men are cut down by the sword, as corn by the sickle, Jer. 51. 33. 4. For boughs, which trees put forth, as the earth puts out corn in harvest, Isai. 27. 11. Psal. 80. 11. So here, When this root is watered, though it seemed dead before, it shall send forth abundance of boughs, as the earth does corn in harvest.

*{like a plant}* As if it were new planted, or as a tree new planted, or as another plant; to wit, that was never cut down. It sends out boughs so plentifully, that you can see no difference between it, and another tree that remained untouched. See the like addition, Chap. 8. 12.

**V. 10. ***{But man dieth and wasteth away; yea, man giveth up the ghost, and where is he?}* Here is the other part of the dissimilitude expressed with a pathetical interrogation. A man’s condition is worse than a tree’s: It may be cut down to the ground, and grow again in the same place, and your eyes may see it. But man dying, is plucked up by the roots, and carried clean out of the world, and who can tell what is become of him? There is not so much as any memorial of him left here, Chap. 7. 8, 9, 10.

*{But}* Heb. *And*: Yet it is often so translated, and must be so here, because it ushers in an unlikeness between a man and a tree. So this particle ו is used, Prov. 5. 4. & 8. 36. and wheresoever it is used to set out a dissimilitude.

*{man}* A name taken from strength, as is *Enosh* from infirmity. See on Chap. 3. 3. & 4. 17. & 10. 5.

*{dieth}* Although he be never so strong, yet he must die. Death spares none.

*{and}* Some read it, *when*. So this particle ו is used, Psal. 139. 16. 1 Sam. 13. 1. And they put his wasting away before his death, as the cause of it. But it is better read, *And*. It is to be understood, as a consequent of his death, making him unable to appear any more; and so it agrees best with the end of the verse.

*{wasteth away}* Or, *is weakened*. So the word signifies, Isa. 14. 12. Joel 3. 10. And the meaning is, that all his strength being taken away by death, his weak body wastes away, and consumes in the grave. Or, *is cut off*. As some translate it in Exod. 17. 13. Isa. 14. 12. He is cut off worse than a tree; for he grows no more. The word is translated by our interpreters, *discomfiting*, or *being overcome*, Exod. 17. 13. & 32. 18. and it holds well with the scope here. For as men overcome, are carried away captives from their own land, never to return thither again, so are men overcome by death carried clean out of this world.

*{yea}* So it is translated, Chap. 21. 19. Some understand it thus; If man be killed by some violent death, he wastes away; yea, though he should die but a natural death, and gently give up the ghost, yet he appears no more here in the world. Others read it, *And*. They conceive that the two words, of dying, and giving up the ghost, knit together by this particle, signify the same thing, and serve only to set out the copiousness of the tongue, and the certainty of his utter extirpation out of this world by death.

*{man}* See on verse 1.

*{giveth up the ghost}* See on Chap. 3. 11. & 10. 18.

*{and where is he?}* He is not to be found anywhere among the living. See on Chap. 7. 8. & 3. 16.

**V. 11. ***{As the waters fail from the Sea, and the flood decayeth and drieth up}* Having before by a dissimilitude set out man’s hopelessness of returning into this world after death, and showed his condition to be worse than a tree’s cut down; now he goes on to set out man’s nonappearance after death, by an elegant similitude of the drying up of waters.

*{As}* This word is not in the original; and therefore some make this a dissimilitude also, and read this verse by way of interrogation. Do the waters fail from the sea, or the flood decay and dry up? yet man lies down, and rises not, &c. Intimating, that man is in a worse condition than the sea and rivers, for they keep their place still, and are to be seen, where they were; but men drop out of the earth one after another, and appear here no more. But the former interpretation by way of similitude is better.

*{the waters}* See on verse 9.

*{fail}* Heb. *are gone*. See the word in this sense, 1 Sam. 9. 7. Deut. 32. 36.

*{from the sea}* The word ים signifies, 1. Any great place of water, or lake; as Gen. 1. 10. Numb. 34. 11. The sea of Cinnareth is thought to be the lake of Gennezareth. 2. The sea itself, the greatest receptacle of water. Either the Mediterranean, Josh. 23. 4. or the Red Sea, Psal. 114. 3. 3. The west, because the great sea stood westward from Jerusalem, Gen. 12. 8. 4. The south, because the sea stood southward from Jerusalem, Psal. 107. 3. 5. For a great vessel made to hold much water, 1 King. 7. 23.

*{and the flood}* A stream having store of water in it, and running swiftly.

*{decayeth and dryeth up}* Two words of one signification, to add emphasis to it, and set out a total drying up. See the like on verse 10. 1 Pet. 1. 7. Some understand it thus; As the sea or river receive not the same water back again, that is drawn out of them by vapors, or overflows the earth, so man dying, returns no more into the world. But the truth of the similitude thus explained, may be doubted of. Others thus; as a lake, or a river, having broken into another channel, returns no more into the former, but leaves it dry and desolate; so man once dead, returns hither no more: this is better. But the best is to read it thus; *As when the waters from the sea fail, the floud decayeth, and dryeth up. So, &c.* verse 12. And the sense is, that as rivers coming from the sea are quite dried up, when the sea waters fail, that feed them; and no water appears any more there: so when man’s life is taken away, it returns no more, while this world lasts. See on verse 10. And ו is so used, Prov. 11. 10.

**V. 12. ***{So man lieth down, and riseth not; till the heavens be no more, they shall not awake, nor be raised out of their sleep.}* This verse is a bridge, or a transition from one matter to another. In the beginning of the verse, he applies the former similitude, to show, that man being once dead, returns no more into this world to dwell here again among the sons of men. In the end of the verse, lest any should mistake his words, and think him an atheist, that denies the resurrection, he does as it were recall himself, and grant a resurrection at the end of the world: and like a man in an ague, follows that matter in a good temper, till, verse 16. looking to his present pressures again, he falls into another hot fit of unruly passion, which lasts to the end of the chapter.

*{so}* Heb. *And*. But it is often rendered *so*; as Prov. 25. 20, 23, 25, 27.

*{men}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{lieth down}* Death is set out here by the similitude of one lying down to sleep: and the grave is as the bed. The word שכב signifies, 1. To lie down on the ground, Numb. 24. 9. 2. To lay himself down to sleep, Deut. 6. 7. 3. To die; for death is like to sleep, Deut. 31. 16. 4. To be sick; for such lie in bed, Psal. 41. 8. 5. To lie with a woman by way of carnal copulation, Gen. 26. 10. Here it is taken in the third sense for dying.

*{and riseth not}* To wit, to live again in the earth among men. For Job did believe the resurrection, and confesses it in the words following; as also, Chap. 19. 27, &c. See on ver. 7, 10. The word קום in Scripture signifies, 1. To rise, 1 King. 8. 54. 2. To come forth, or appear, Deut. 34. 10. 3. To stand, Josh. 7. 12. 4. To prevail, Psal. 1. 5. 5. To remain, Josh. 2. 11. 6. To be firm or stable, Numb. 30. 5. 7. To stand still, or be useless, 1 Sam. 4. 15. His eyes were dim. Heb. *stood still*. Here it is taken in the first sense with the former limitation of not rising to live here.

*{till the heavens be no more}* These words are to be knit to the words following, They shall not awake till the heavens be no more. Some interpret it, never; to wit, by their own strength, or to live here; because the heavens remain forever in their substance, though they shall be changed in their qualities at the end of the world. But in regard that Job in the words following, falls upon the point of the resurrection, the words are rather to be understood thus. They shall not rise till the general resurrection, when these heavens shall be changed and renewed, Psal. 102. 25, 26. Isa. 65. 17. & 66. 22. 2 Pet. 3. 7, 10, 11. Rev. 21. 1. For the word, *Heavens*, see on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. It may here be meant of the starry, cloudy, and airy heavens.

*{they shall not awake}* He changes the number here. Before he had said, He rises not; now he says, They shall not awake: lest any should think he had spoken of some particular man only. He therefore now speaks it of all men. By awaking here, is meant rising from the sleep of death; as appears by the words following.

*{nor be raised}* Lest anyone should object, It is true, the dead cannot awake of themselves; but God can raise them up again, when he will, as men in a sleep may be waked by others, before they would awake of themselves, he adds, that God will not raise them neither till the resurrection.

*{out of their sleep}* From death to life. It is no unusual thing in Scripture, to compare death to sleep. See on Chap. 3. 13. Sleep is put sometime for the sleep of nature, Gen. 28. 11. Sometime for the sleep of sin, Eph. 5. verse 14. Sometime for the sleep of death, as Chap. 3. 13. and so here: and Dan. 12. 2.

**V. 13. ***{O that thou wouldest hide me in the grave, that thou wouldest keep me secret, until thy wrath be past, that thou wouldest appoint me a set time, and remember me.}* Job now being fallen upon the consideration of the resurrection, wishes for death, that in the grave he might be free from his pains, and afterwards enjoy a happy resurrection.

*{O that thou wouldest}* For the Hebrew words, and their manner of wishing, see on Chap. 6. 8.

*{hide me}* He compares himself to a man pursued by his enemies; and desires, that God would hide him from them; that is, that he would free him from his sore pains, that followed him so close, that he could by no means escape them.

*{in the grave}* See on Chap. 7. 9.

*{that thou wouldest keep me secret}* The same thing desired in other words. See on verse 11. It shows Job’s desire to be earnest: as if one running from an enemy should say, Pray hide me, pray hide me: Be sure to keep me safe.

*{until thy wrath}* The word אף signifies, 1. The face, 1 Sam. 25. 23. 2. The nose, which is the highest part of the face, Cant. 7. 4. 3. Anger, which is showed by the high color of the face, and thick breathing of the nose. So it is taken here, for anger. See more on Chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5, 13.

*{be past}* Heb. *turned back*; to wit, into gentleness, and kindness; as Psal. 6. 4. or *turned away*; as Isa. 12. 1. that is, Be quite and clean gone, and taken away from me. The word שוב signifies, 1. To return to a place, from which one went away before, Gen. 18. 33. 2. To return from anger, or to be friends again, Jer. 2. 35. 3. To repent, or return to God, from whom we have all naturally gone astray, Deut. 30. 2. 4. To do the same thing again, Gen. 26. 18. Isaac dug again. Heb. *returned and dug*. 5. To make to return, or bring back, Deut. 30. 3. Here it is taken in the second sense. Till you cease to be angry with me. Till the resurrection, when all your wrath will be gone from me.

*{that thou wouldest appoint me}* Heb. *that thou wouldest put for me*; or, *set for me*. He knew that the time of his lying in the grave was determined from eternity, verse 5. but his desire is, that this appointed time might come quickly, that he might be at ease, and sleep quietly in hope of a joyful resurrection. See the like wish, Psal. 39. 4.

*{a set time}* Heb. *a statute*. A certain time, when, and how long I shall lie in the grave.

*{and remember me}* How God is said to remember, see on Chap. 7. 7. & 10. 9. Here Job’s meaning is, that God would raise him up again, for God seems to forget his people while he leaves them in trouble, in prison, in the grave, Psal. 88. 5. and he is said to remember them, when he delivers, or eases them, Gen. 8. 1. Exod. 2. 24. Others read it, *That thou wouldest appoint me a set time, when thou wilt remember me*; to wit, to raise me out of the grave. For ו *when*, see on verse 10.

**V. 14. ***{If a man die, shall he live again? all the days of my appointed time will I wait, till my change come}* Job having now refreshed his soul with a sweet meditation of the resurrection, stands admiring at that glorious work, and concludes from thence a willingness to be dissolved.

*{if}* Or, *when*; as it is translated, Gen. 38. 9. Num. 36. 4. For there is no doubt of man’s dying at all.

*{a man}* See on verse 10. & Chap. 3. 3. & 4. 17. & 10. 5.

*{die}* See on verse 10.

*{shall he live again?}* Some understand it as a flat denial, as affirmative questions usually are interpreted in Scripture; and they give this sense. Certainly he shall not live any more here; as ver. 10, 12. Else I would be patient till I die, hoping to be raised to a better state here. But it is rather spoken by way of admiration. Has God said, he will raise up our bodies again? then I will willingly die in hope of a glorious resurrection. See the like question, Chap. 15. 11. Gen. 3. 1. *Hath God said*, &c. It is strange he should envy you the eating of so precious a fruit; and Gen. 17. 17. *Shall a child be born*, &c. It is very strange, that a man and woman so old should have a child: Our divines take it to be a voice of joy, and admiration; not of doubting, as Sara’s was: and therefore she is reproved, Gen. 18. 13. but Abraham is not blamed. So Chap. 4. 2. *If we assay to commune with thee, wilt thou be grieved?* It is strange you should be troubled with the words of your friends; yet we believe you will. See on Chap. 21. 17.

*{all the days}* The word יום signifies, 1. A natural day of 24. hours, comprehending the night also, Esth. 4. 16. 2. An artificial day of 12. hours, opposed to the night, Esth. 4. 16. 3. A great day, or a year of days, containing all days in it. Judg. 17. 10. 4. A certain time consisting of many days, Isa. 4. 1. 5. A joyful, or sad time, Psal. 37. 13. Isa. 13. 22. Here it is taken in the fourth sense, for the whole time of his life.

*{of my appointed time}* See on Chap. 7. 1.

*{will I wait}* Or, *expect*, Chap. 32. 16. I will gladly look for death every day, and be joyful, if the time were come.

*{till my change}* Till the time of my death, which is indeed a great change. A living body is changed into a dead carcass. Man goes out of this world to another, Prov. 31. 8. appointed to destruction. Heb. children of change. Or, *Till my passing away*, Or, *departure*. For death is such a change, that it carries a man clean out of the world, Eccl. 1. 4. Cant. 2. 11. 2 Tim. 4. 6. Others understand it of his resurrection, which indeed is a great change: but how could Job wait for that in the grave?

*{come}* For living actions attributed to dead things, see on Chapter 1. 19.

**V. 15. ***{Thou shalt call, and I will answer thee: thou wilt have a desire to the work of thine hands.}* The same words, as here in the beginning of this verse, that were in Chap. 13. 22. but to a far different purpose. For there Job in an impatient fit, desires to plead with God, as appears by his following plea there. But here in a calm temper, he proposes his hope, to find mercy with God in the resurrection, as appears by the end of the verse.

*{thou shalt call}* You shall call me out of the grave, by the voice of Christ, or the archangel’s voice at the end of the world, Joh. 5. 28, 29. 1 Thess. 4. 16. For the various significations of the word, see on Chap. 13. 22.

*{and I will answer thee}* Or, *I shall answer thee*. Not that there is any readiness in dead bodies to come out of the grave, nor that they shall be called everyone by name, but they shall come up suddenly at Christ’s call, as a servant comes to his master at his call suddenly: and this is a real answer. See the like of hearing, Hos. 2. 21, 22.

*{thou wilt have a desire}* You will be very willing to see it again, after it has lain so long in the grave, as a workman having been long absent, is willing to see what he has built, or planted. Hence comes כסף, silver, which men are very desirous to look upon.

*{to the work of thine hands}* To me, who am made by you. Though the work of your hands seem to be blotted out in the grave; yet you will show, the love which moved you to form me at the first, is not clean extinct, but shall appear in raising me up gloriously at last. See more on Chap. 10. ver. 3, 8.

**V. 16. ***{For now thou numberest my steps: dost thou not watch over my sin?}* It will be well with me then, when I shall be raised up again; though now you seem to deal very hardly with me. Here Job begins another impatient fit, which holds him to the end of the chapter.

*{for}* Or, *but*; as it is translated, Chap. 9. 9. 18. For here is an opposition of what went before, and not a confirmation of it.

*{now}* As it goes with me now, it seems so, that you take all advantages to punish me.

*{thou numberest}* You keep an exact account of every sin of mine, that you may take occasion to punish me for the same.

*{my steps}* Every step I fetch; that is, every sin I commit. See Chap. 31. 4. & 34. 21. Psal. 56. 6. & 139. 2, 3, 4. Prov. 5. 21.

*{dost thou not watch over my sin?}* That you may be sure to number aright, and miss no one sin; you do watch all I do to take advantage against me. See Chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14. & 13. 27. And for the word, sin, see Chap. 5. 24. & 7. 20. & 10. 6.

**V. 17. ***{My transgression is sealed up in a bag, and thou sowest up mine iniquity.}* He goes on to describe the sharpness of God’s proceedings against him, who does not only watch what he does amiss, and number all his errors, but also keep them all fast in his mind, that none of them may be forgotten, and sew them together, that he may punish him for all together.

*{my transgression}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{is sealed up}* Shut up fast, that no man may take it away, before I be punished for it. See on Chap. 9. 7.

*{in a bag}* The word comes from צרר to bind, because those things which we would keep safe, and not lose, we bind up in a bag, and then seal it for the greater safety.

*{and thou sowest up mine iniquity}* You will be sure to make it fast. We think tying a bag, and sealing it, not to be sufficient, when we would keep things very safe, and therefore we sew up the bag, that nothing may fall out. Hereby is meant God’s exact care, not to forget any one sin of Job’s, but to be sure to keep all in mind, to punish him the more severely. Or, *And thou sowest it unto mine iniquity*. That is, You join my former transgressions, to my present, to be sure to punish me for all. Or, You join my sin to my punishment. So the word signifies, Isai. 56. 6. Or, *Thou sowest it over mine iniquity*, to wit, the bag for the word *sowing*, see on Chap. 13. 4. For the word *iniquity*, see on Chap. 7. 21. As men sew one piece of cloth to another, to make a complete garment; so do you call to mind all mine iniquities, at once, to lay load on me, Chap. 13. 26. Isa. 65. ver. 6, 7.

**V. 18. ***{And surely the mountain falling cometh to nought and the rock is removed out of his place.}* In this verse, by two similitudes of the decay of mountains, and rocks, the highest and firmest things, he further sets out man’s mortality, and impossibility of returning into this world again. In the next by two others, by the decay of hard stones, and growing things. The application begins at the end of the next verse.

*{and surely}* See on Chap. 1. 11.

*{the mountain falling}* Falling away by little and little, by reason of some earthquake, or fiery mines under it, or the sea underlaking it. See more on Chap. 9. 5. Some read it, *as* the mountains falling, &c. so you destroy the hope of men, ver. 19. Thus *as*, is added, ver. 11.

*{cometh to nought}* Heb. *fadeth*. It molders and withers away like a leaf fallen off a tree, Psal. 1. 3.

*{and the rock is removed out of his place}* Either by old age decaying; as the word also signifies, Psal. 6. 7. or by the means mentioned before. See on Chap. 9. 5.

**V. 19. ***{The waters wear the stones: thou washest away the things which grow out of the dust of the earth, and thou destroyest the hope of man.}* As in the former verse he had showed man’s mortality, and irrecoverableness into this world after death, by the decay of highest and firmest things, hills and rocks; so in this verse, by the irreparable ruin of the hardest, and best-rooted things; as stones, and things growing out of the earth, which one would think, lay very safe, and had a sure foundation for continuance.

*{the waters}* It may be understood either of rainwater, or waters here below. For the various significations of the word, see on verse 9.

*{wear}* By often falling upon them, or continual running over them they diminish them.

*{the stones}* The word אבן is taken, 1. for ordinary stones, such as lie on the ground, Chap. 5. 23. 2. a vessel made of stone, Exod. 7. 19. 3. an idol of stone, Jer. 2. 27. 4. a stone to weigh things by, for such were the Jews’ weights, Prov. 11. 1. See the margin there. 5. a plummet, Zech. 4. 10. See the margin there. Here it is taken in the first sense. Stones are hard and strong, Chap. 6. 12. yet water will wear them away in time.

*{thou washest away}* Or, *overflowest*. He turns his speech to God, to whom he attributes these devastations. So ver. 20. *Thou prevailest*, &c. See the like. Gen. 49. 18. To God he spoke before, verse 16, 17. and so continues. Others read it, *The dust of the earth overflowes the things which grow out of it*; That is, the sand and rubbish which a flood brings, does drown and kill things growing out of the earth. Others read it, *The drops thereof wear the dust of the earth*; That is, they do in time not only wear away stones, but the earth itself by little and little. The first reading is the best.

*{the things which grow}* Heb. *which grow out of it*. It signifies such things as naturally grow out of the earth without plowing or sowing, or arise out of corn scattered the year before, Lev. 25. 5. 2 King. 19. 29. Isa. 37. 30. one would think the earth should preserve its own brood, yet it cannot.

*{out of the dust of the earth}* Out of the earth itself, which is nothing but dust congealed, and crumbles to dust. See on verse 8. & Chap. 9. 24.

*{and}* Or, *so*. See on ver. 18. Here is the reddition of the similitude. It is translated, *so*, ver. 12.

*{thou destroyest}* Heb. *thou makest to perish*. Though a man in prosperity think he shall abide so, yet you do disappoint him.

*{the hope}* His hope of continuing longer in this world, or of ever returning into it again. You put him quite out of hope by killing him. For the word, see on Chap. 5. 16. & 7. 6. & 11. 18.

*{of man}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 20. ***{Thou prevailest for ever against him, and he passeth: thou changest his countenance, and sendest him away.}* He goes on in the application of the former similitude, and shows the manner how God disappoints the hope of man, to wit, by sending him out of the world by his mighty power.

*{Thou prevailest}* You are stronger than he, and so prevail against him by your strength, and overcome him.

*{for ever}* So that you shut him forever out of this world by death. See on Chap. 4. 20.

*{against him}* Against sorry and weak man, mentioned ver. 19. And what great victory is this for the mighty God to use his great power to overcome a weak man?

*{and he passeth}* As a soldier overcome in battle by a potent adversary flies away, and is seen no more there, so does man out of the world, being overcome by God. It intimates a violent, or at least an untimely death. The word הלך signifies, 1. Going about something, though a man walk not; as Chap. 1. 4. 2. Going on in a continued way, or walking, Gen. 51. 19. 3. The passage of ships and other lifeless things, Psal. 104. 26. 4. Passing out of the world, or dying, Gen. 15. 15. 5. Constancy in a condition, as walking childless, Gen. 15. 2. 6. It imports a course of life good or bad, Psal. 1. 1. & 119. 1. Here it is taken in the fourth sense, for passing out of the world by death, and that by a violent one also, as was noted before. See on Chap. 10. 21.

*{thou changest}* Or, *changing*. For it will agree with the former words, *He passeth changing his countenance*. But it is better to attribute this change to God, to whom sending him out of the world, is attributed in the end of the verse. The word שנה signifies sometimes to do the same thing, or double; as Gen. 41. 32. 2. To do another thing quite different from it, or to alter or change a thing; as Mal. 3. 6. So here.

*{his countenance}* The word פנים signifies, 1. The countenance of a living creature, Isa. 25. 8. 2. The outside of a thing, which is visible like the face of man, Chap. 5. 10. 3. The person, the face being a principal part of the body, and a chief ornament of the person, chap. 13. 10. 4. The credit or reputation, which is open and public like the face, Chap. 9. 24. 5. The presence, which is discerned by the face, Chap. 1. 12. 6. The demonstration of anger or sadness, or cheerfulness, which use to appear in the face, Gen. 31. 2. 1 Sam. 1. 18. Chap. 9. 27. Here it is taken in the first sense. Death takes away the beauty of man, and makes his countenance pale and wan.

*{and sendest him away}* This word שלח has many and various significations, 1. To send a messenger, Chap. 1. 4, 5. 2. To put forth the hand, Gen. 3. 22. Chap. 1. 11. 3. To hold out the finger, or point to one, Isa. 58. 9. 4. To pour out, Chap. 5. 10. 5. To speak or send out words, Psal. 50. 19. 6. To send away, Gen. 3. 23. 7. To send forth, Psal. 43. 3. 8. To let the hair grow, and not to cut it, Ezek. 44. 20. Here it is used in the sixth sense. God by death sends a man clean away out of this world into another.

**V. 21. ***{His sons come to honour, and he knoweth it not; and they are brought low; but he perceiveth it not of them.}* In this verse he proves, that man by death is clean cast out of the world, because he knows nothing of what is done here, no not that which concerns such as once were most near and dear unto him.

*{His sons}* Or, *If his sons*. If they be preferred, or brought low, he is ignorant of it. He speaks of the dead man’s sons mentioned in the former verse. The word בן signifies, 1. A son by nature, Prov. 1. 1. and nephew or son’s son, Gen. 29. 5. Laban is called the son of Nahor, but was his grandchild. 2. An adopted son, Exod. 2. 10. 3. A child, whether male, or female, Gen. 3. 16. 4. A scholar is called the son of the teacher, 2 King. 2. 3. 5. An inhabitant is called a son, as if the place or city were his mother, Isa. 11. 14. 6. A thing contained in another, as the child in the womb, Lam. 3. 13. 7. One belonging or destinated to anything, Psal. 102. 20. 8. The young ones of birds or beasts are called sons, Lev. 1. 14. 9. To set out time or age, Gen. 17. 1. *Abram was ninety years old*, Heb. *a son of ninety years*. Here it is taken in the first sense, for his sons and posterity.

*{come to honour}* Or, *are weighty*. Loaded with wealth, and honor, Isa. 66. 5.

*{and he knoweth it not}* The dead father can take no notice of it. So the word is used, Chap. 5. 10. & 9. 21. Isa. 63. 16. Eccl. 2. 19.

*{and they are brought low}* Heb. *are small*, yet not meant, that they are small in number, but poor and contemptible; as appears by opposition to the former part of the verse, and by the use of the word elsewhere, Chap. 8. 7. Psal. 119. 141. Jer. 30. 19.

*{but he perceiveth it not of them}* He is so clean estranged from all human affairs, that he knows not what becomes of his own children, whether they be rich or poor, high or low. Some think it an answer to his friends’ words, Chap. 5. 25. that if he would repent, his posterity should prosper. Nay says Job, that is no encouragement; for a dead man knows not what becomes of his posterity, and therefore neither can receive comfort from their prosperity, nor sorrow from their ruin.

**V. 22. ***{But his flesh upon him shall have pain, and his soule within him shall mourn.}* The scope of this verse is to show, that dead men have no communion at all with those that are alive, and that all bands of nature are ceased, when men are dead.

*{But}* Or, *Onely*. If any man ask what he does when he is dead, this is all that can be said of him, that he takes no care for any left behind in the world.

*{His flesh}* The word בשר is taken; 1. for the soft part of man’s body, opposed to bones, Gen. 2. 23. 2. for the whole body, opposed to the soul, Gen. 3. 24. 3. for any bodily living creature, Gen. 7. 16. 4. for the whole man, Deut. 5. 26. 5. for a kinsman according to the flesh, Gen. 37. 27. 6. for a weak, or frail thing not to be leaned on, and therein like to flesh without bones, Jer. 17. 5. Isa. 31. 1, 3. 7. for a thing soft and tractable, like flesh, Ezek. 36. 26. 8. for the corruption of man’s nature, Gen. 6. 3. 9. for the private members, Gen. 17. 23. 10. for the skin, which is of a fleshly substance, and covers the flesh, Psal. 102. 5. Here it is taken in the second sense, for it is opposed to the soul.

*{upon him}* Or, *for it selfe*: Or, *concerning itself*. It is said to be upon him, because it is the visible part of man; as in the end of the verse, the soul, which is the invisible part, is said to be within him.

*{shall have pain}* The word כאב signifies; 1. Pain of body, Gen. 34. 25. 2. Grief of mind, which oftentimes arises out of bodily pain, by reason of the sympathy between soul and body, Prov. 14. 13. 3. The marring or spoiling of a thing, because, both the things men grieve for, and grief itself, mar the body, and corrupt it. The translators here take it in the first sense: but the third agrees better with the nature of a dead body, which is capable of corruption, but not of pain.

*{and his soule}* The word נפש signifies; 1. Breath, Chap. 41. 21. 2. Life, Gen. 9. 4, 5. 3. The soul, without which there is neither life nor breath in the body, Gen. 2. 7. 4. a living body, which has a soul in it, Gen. 1. 20, 24. 5. a man, Exod. 1. 5. 6. man’s body, Psal. 105. 18. He was laid in iron, Heb. *His soul*. As in the margin there, yet it was the body of Joseph only, for his soul could not be laid in iron. 7. a dead carcass, that had once a soul in it, Lev. 19. 28. & 22. 4. For the dead, Heb. *for a soul*. 8. For the heart, mind, or desire of a man, Exod. 23. 9. 9. for the person, as for God himself, Amos 6. 8. Here it is taken in the third sense, for the soul itself, for it is opposed to the flesh.

*{within him}* It is the same word, that was used before, for, *upon him*, in this verse: but the translators, having rightly observed the difference between soul and body, as was there noted, have fitly translated it here, *within him*. So it is used, Chap. 18. 6. & 8. 16.

*{shall mourn}* The word imports lamentation inward in mind, or outwardly expressed, and sometimes is attributed to senseless things, as to the earth, Isa. 24. 4. Jer. 12. 4. because they are in an ill condition, and are uncomely, and strangely altered in their color, or ruined, as men that mourn. So if the soul be taken for the life here, it may be meant, that as the body is corrupted, so the life is lost. Some understand it of the man alive, and render the sense thus, He was troubled while he was alive with his own misery, and grieved for his own troubles; but now he takes no notice of what befalls his posterity. Others, understand it of the dead man, set out like one alive, and brought in complaining of, and mourning for his sorrows; but no more intended by it, but that he is not affected with any good or evil that befalls others; yea, like a hard-hearted man, regards not his own posterity. So living acts are attributed to dead men, Chap. 21. 23. & 3. 17, 18, 19. & 17. 13, 14. Isa. 14. 9, 10. This latter sense is very emphatical, and agrees best with the scope of Job in this place.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1. ***{Then answered Eliphaz the Temanite, and said}* For this verse see on Chap. 4. 1. & 2. 11. & 6. 1. & 16. 1. The first conflict of Job with his three friends being ended, the second begins here, and ends at the end of Chap. 21. No new matter is added, but they confirm what they had said before, and charge Job openly. Eliphaz had dealt more closely before, in general terms only, accusing Job to be wicked, because none but wicked men were so punished. Now he charges him more particularly, as appears in the very beginning of his speech, verse 2, &c. His charge against Job is in the 16. first verses of this chapter; and at the 17. verse, and so on to the end of the chapter, he goes on with his old theme to prove, that not good men, but wicked men are scourged and destroyed by God’s horrible judgments. And it is to be observed, that both Eliphaz, and Job’s other two friends, in the second conflict, angered peradventure, by some harsh words of Job, do only lay load upon him, as upon a wicked man, but afford him not one exhortation to repentance, or one comfortable promise, as formerly they had done, Chap. 5. 17. & 8. 20. & 11. 13. as if they utterly despaired of his ever returning unto God.

**V. 2. ***{Should a wise man utter vain knowledge, and fill his belly with the East-wind?}* Eliphaz having heard all that Job had spoken, What, says he, Does it become him, who would seem to be so wise, and well advised, to utter words that are vain, and lighter than the wind itself, as if they contained great wisdom? Yea, like the east wind, to blow hurtful words out of his swelling breast? What he speaks here by a similitude, he declares in plain terms in the next verse, and comes to the particular charge at the fourth verse.

*{Should}* He speaks like one in choler, in a chiding way. Are you not ashamed to talk thus? Does it become you? Certainly it does not. Such pathetical questions are frequent in this book, Chap. 4. 2. & 8. 2. & 11. 2, 3. They are the common beginnings of the speeches of Job’s friends, and argue much choler and confidence.

*{a wise man}* As you think yourself to be. For it appears, that he meant Job, verse 4. though he would not speak particularly to him at first. There he charges him home, and declares, what these vain words were.

*{utter}* Heb. *answer*. See on Chap. 4. 1. & 6. 1.

*{vain knowledge}* Heb. *knowledge of the wind*. The word רוח signifies; 1. the wind, Chap. 1. 19. 2. vanity, a thing of no substance, but empty like the wind, Eccl. 5. 16. 3. some one corner or quarter of the earth, where the wind sometimes blows, 1 Chron. 9. 24. 4. the breath of a living creature, which is a kind of wind, Psal. 135. 17. 5. a spirit, or angel, Chap. 4. 15. 6. the soul of man, Gen. 45. 27. 7. the life of a living creature, which cannot be without soul or breath, Chap. 10. 12. 8. the Holy Ghost, the third person in Trinity, Gen. 1. 2. 9. the gifts of the Holy Ghost, Joel 2. 28. Here it is taken in the second sense, for vain and empty knowledge, as the translation shows. Such knowledge as is good for nothing, nor gives no satisfaction, as ver. 3. Chap. 6. 26. & 7. 7. & 16. 3. Prov. 11. 29. Jer. 5. 13. So light account had they of Job’s words.

*{and fill}* The Hebrew word מלא when it is spoken of a vessel, or place, it signifies filling, Gen. 1. 28. & 42. 25. when it has relation to a time or word, it signifies fulfilling, Gen. 29. 21, 27. 1 King. 2. 27. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{his belly}* בטן signifies; 1. the belly itself of a living creature, Chap. 1. 21. 2. the bowels or entrails that are in the belly, Numb. 5. 22. 3. the middle of anything, whereby the upper and lower parts are joined together, as in man by the belly, 1 King. 7. 20. Here it is taken in the first sense in the similitude; but in the application, hereby is meant the heart, or soul, filled with frothy knowledge, ver. 35. Chap. 20. 20. & 32. 19. Prov. 20. 27.
