For the soul receives things, and digests them, in point of knowledge, as the belly does meat.

*{with the East-wind}* The word comes from קדם: *before*; coming as it were from the face, or forepart of heaven, from the sunrising, not from the back parts, or west, where the sun sets, and leaves darkness behind it. Hereby is meant devising false and dangerous things, to feed their own fancies, and utter to the hurt of others. For the east wind is destructive to herbs and fruits, Hos. 12. 1. Gen. 41. 6.

**V. 3. ***{Should he reason with unprofitable talk? or with speeches wherewith he can do no good?}* In this verse he endeavors to make good the former part of his charge, closely intimating, that Job’s words were empty like wind: and in the next verse he plainly charges him with destructive words, like the east wind, that so he might confirm the latter part of his charge, in the former verse.

*{Should he}* See on ver. 2.

*{reason}* The word יכח signifies, 1. To reason a case by answers and replies, Chap. 6. 25. 2. To convince, confute or reprove such as are in the wrong. Chap. 6. 26. 3. To correct or scourge one for sin, Psal. 6. 1. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{with unprofitable}* Heb. *that profits not*. He charges Job with using weak arguments, unable to prove the thing in question.

*{talk}* Heb. *words*. The Hebrew word דבר is used, 1. For a word, Hos. 1. 1. 2. For a thing; for as it is expressed by words, so it expresses a man’s meaning, when it is done, 1 King. 17. 17. 1 Sam. 15. 13, 14. Here it is taken in the first sense for words used by Job in disputing with his friends: yet not for a bare word, but for sentences. So the ten commandments are called ten words, Exod. 34. 28. See the margin there.

*{or with speeches, wherewith he can do no good}* Bring no profit. Not be able to prove his cause, or do any good to others, but rather seduce them. The same thing uttered in other words for the more assurance, and to express the greater vehemency. See on Chap. 14. 10, 11.

**V. 4. ***{Yea, thou castest off fear, and restrainest prayer before God}* You do by consequence teach men to cast off all religion, and worship of God, as a thing that cannot profit them, because you maintain, that God deals as ill with good men, as with bad, Chap. 9. 22, 23. Job’s friends speaking by turns, answer sometimes to what Job said to their fellows, as it came to their mind, as well as to what he said to themselves.

*{yea}* You do not only speak vainly, but also wickedly, which is worse. Or, *How much more dost thou cast off fear?* How much more wickedly do you, in giving other men grounds of impiety? This is far worse, than speaking vanity. See on Chap. 4. 19.

*{thou dost cast off}* Both from yourself, and others. Heb. *Thou makest void*. Besides the person included in the verb, the pronoun is added in the original to brand Job the more, as if he had said; None but such an one as you, would have spoken so vilely. The word פרר here used signifies, 1. To break a thing asunder, or divide it, Psal. 74. 13. 2. To make void, and of none effect, Numb. 30. 8. So here. Now they that cast off the fear of God from themselves, or others, do as much as lies in them, make it void, Psal. 119. 126.

*{fear}* The fear of God: As the word, for the word of God, Jam. 1. 21. 1 Pet. 2. 2. So the law and testimony, Isa. 8. 20. For these are most excellent in their kind, and therefore men will with most reason guess, them to be intended, though God’s name be not added to them. יראה is taken, 1. for a fear of sinning against God, Exod. 14. 31. 2. for a fear of reverence, Mal. 3. 16. 3. for the true religion, 2 King. 17. 25. Job 1. 1. So it is taken here; as appears by the end of the verse. You will make men not to care for serving God at all, seeing no good is to be gotten by it, Mal. 3. 14, 15. You will destroy all religion.

*{and restrainest}* Discourage men from praying by your ungodly words.

*{prayer}* The word שיחה signifies, 1. meditation, Psal. 104. 34. 2. a prayer or complaint, which proceeds out of meditation, Psal. 102. in the title, Job 7. 13. So it is used here, and cast in Job’s dish as a great fault (as indeed it were, had it been true) that he hindered men from praying to God.

*{before God}* See on Chap. 8. 5.

**V. 5. ***{For thy mouth uttereth thine iniquity, and thou chusest the tongue of the crafty.}* I need not prove the former charge, that you discourage men from the worship of God, for your own words have sufficiently discovered it.

*{For thy mouth}* The word, פה 1. signifies that member which we call the mouth of the body, Psal. 115. 5. 2. The speech, or words, that come out of the mouth, Gen. 41. 40. *Thy word*, Heb. *thy mouth*. 3. The round top of a well, or garment, or vessel, which is like a mouth, Gen. 29. 2. 4. The edge of a sword, which devours, or destroys men, as the mouth does meat, Gen. 34. 26. 5. The end or corner of a place, 2 King. 10. 21. 6. A part or portion, as the mouth is a part of the face, Deut. 21. 17. Here it is taken in the second sense, for the words that come out of Job’s mouth; as the tongue is also used in this verse, and the lips in the next. For these are the instruments of speaking; and if Job had said nothing, his mouth had not accused him.

*{uttereth}* Heb. *teacheth*. The word אלף signifies, 1. by speech, to instruct others; as Chap. 33. 33. 2. to show one something, he saw not, or observed not before; as Exod. 15. 25. So here, your speech bewrays this evil purpose of your heart. I gather it out of your own words. I need no other witnesses. Your own mouth has uttered it. See on ver. 4.

*{thine iniquity}* See on Chap. 7. 21. Others read it thus; *For thine iniquity teacheth thy mouth.* You could not speak so wickedly, if you had not a very rotten heart. But the former reading agrees best with the coherence of this verse with the former: as also with the following verse.

*{and thou chusest}* Or, *though thou chusest*. You do not directly speak against piety, but do closely undermine it by your words. See on ver. 4. For ו *though*: see 2 Chr. 30. 19. The meaning is, You delight to speak craftily, rather than plainly.

*{the tongue}* This word, לשון, signifies; 1. that member, which we call the tongue, Psal. 12. 3. 2. the speech or words; for the tongue is the chief instrument of speaking, Prov. 25. 15. 3. the language of a nation different from others, Esth. 1. 22. 4. A nation that use one tongue or language, Isa. 66. 18. 5. Anything long like a tongue: a wedge of gold, Josh. 7. 21. 6. A bay of the sea that runs out of it like a tongue, narrower to the landward, as the tip of the tongue, put out of the mouth. Josh. 15. 5. Isa. 11. 15. 7. A flame of fire, which is also like a tongue, narrowest at top, Isa. 5. 24. Here it is taken in the second sense for Job’s speech, or words, as the mouth was before in this verse.

*{of the crafty}* See on Chap. 5. 12, 13.

**V. 6. ***{Thine own mouth condemneth thee, and not I; yea, thine own lips testify against thee.}* You may think that I deal too hardly with you, in judging you to be a wicked man, and a hinderer of piety; but I shall need no witnesses to prove it for your own words that have dropped from you, have abundantly proved. See the like, Mat. 26. 65. Luke 19. 22.

*{thine own mouth}* See on ver. 5.

*{condemneth thee}* Of impiety, and hindrance of religion in others. See on chap. 9. 20.

*{and not I}* I shall not need to search for witnesses to condemn you: your own mouth will do it sufficiently. Or I am not your chief condemner, but your own words are. See the like phrase, Joh. 5. 45.

*{yea thine own lips}* This word שפה 1. signifies the lips themselves, Isa. 6. 7. 2. a speech or words, for the lips are instruments of speaking, Prov. 12. 13. 3. a language of a people, Gen. 11. 1. 4. the shore of the sea, Gen. 22. 17. or brink of a river, as the lips to the mouth. Here it is taken for speech, as the mouth and tongue were, ver. 5.

*{testify against thee}* If Job should object, How can my mouth condemn me without witnesses? he answers, You yourself are both witness and judge. Your own tongue guided by the conscience, will both accuse and condemn you. The word ענה signifies 1. to answer to something spoken before, chap. 6. 1. 2. to begin a speech, especially upon occasion of something done before, Chap. 4. 1. 3. to hear prayer; for he that answers it, questionless heard it, 1 Sam. 7. 9. 4. to bear witness; for witnesses ordinarily answer to questions propounded by the judge, Exod. 20. 16. So it is here taken, as the translation shows.

**V. 7. ***{Art thou the first man that was born? or wast thou made before the hills?}* These words seem to take their rise from Job’s words, Chap. 11. 12. wherein he did tacitly intimate, that he thought himself wiser than they, because he was elder. This is with much choler objected to him here by Eliphaz; as if he had said, You do arrogate wisdom to yourself; as if you had been the first man in the world, and by observation of all occurrences, ever since the creation, had gotten singular wisdom to yourself; so that you need not to be taught by any other; but all others had need to learn of you. Thus in an ironical way he goes on to deride Job in this verse, and the next, and concludes the contrary, ver. 9, 10.

*{art thou the first man}* For the *first*, see on chap. 8. 8. It is taken here for the first of all in time. For the word *man*, see on chap. 14. 1.

*{that was born}* The word ילד signifies, 1. to be born; as chap. 14. 1. 2. to be brought forth, for that gives a being to things, as well as birth, Psal. 90. 2. So here. For the first man could not be born, but created. Or, Heb. *And*; yet it is often translated, *or*, as Exod. 21. 15. *and* so it may here; for it differs from the former, *and* expresses something more. For the hills were made before man.

*{wast thou made}* The word חול properly imports the bringing forth of children with pain by the mother, Psal. 51. 5. and thence comes to signify any other bringing forth of a person, or thing, Prov. 8. 24, 25. So here.

*{before the hills}* A proverbial speech, to set out antiquity, or rather eternity; for the hills were made at the beginning of the world, and in likelihood appeared out of the chaos before other parts of the earth; and he that was before the world, must be eternal. Hills being lasting creatures, are used to set out eternity. So much this phrase imports, Prov. 8. 25. Psal. 90. 2. And so much is intimated in the next verse. It is as much as if Eliphaz had said, Do you think that you were before all men, and know more than any man, because of your age? or do you think yet more highly of yourself, that you were from eternity, and know as much as God? These are vain thoughts in you. With the same argument God beats down the pride of Job, in the beginning of his speech to him, Chap. 38. 4, &c.

**V. 8. ***{Hast thou heard the secret of God? and dost thou restrain wisdom to thyself?}* Here he does more fully explain his meaning, that he intended not only to charge Job with a conceit, that he was wiser than all men, but also that he knew God’s determinations from eternity.

*{Hast thou heard}* For the various significations of this word, see on Chap. 13. 17.

*{the secret}* Heb. *in the secret*. That is, have you before the world was, been in God’s private counsel, and heard what was secretly there determined? Jer. 23. 18, 22. One verb included in another. So Ruth 4. 1. *Turn aside*, that is, turn back, and come hither. The word סוד signifies, 1. A thing kept secret, Amos 3. 7. 2. consultation, which uses to be kept secret, lest things agreed upon being made known, may be disappointed, before they can be effected, Psal. 55. 14. 3. An assembly, or council of men met together secretly to consult about matters of weight, Jer. 6. 11. Here it may be taken either in the first, or in the third sense.

*{of God}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{and dost thou restrain wisdom to thyself?}* Do you deny it to all others, and appropriate it to yourself? One verb included in another. See before in this verse, on the word *secret*. The like arrogancy Job had objected to them, Chap. 12. 2.

**V. 9. ***{What knowest thou, that we know not? what understandest thou, that is not in us?}* He proves that Job was not so full of knowledge, as he boasted, nor was not of God’s private counsel, for he knew no more than they. And so confutes Job with his own words, Chap. 12. 3. & 13. 2. For the meaning of this verse, see the notes on those places; for the words differ little, and the sense is the same.

**V. 10. ***{With us are both the gray-headed, and very aged men, much elder then thy father.}* It is likely he has respect to those words of Job, Chap. 12. 12. *with the ancient is wisdom*. You need not (says Eliphaz) bid us mark your words, as things unknown to us, and never heard of before, nor cast your youth in our faces to disgrace us, as if all antiquity were on your side; for we have received from our parents and teachers, who were elder and wiser than you, yea than your father, things more excellent, and more hidden mysteries, than you have told us, and therefore our knowledge is more ancient, and more gray-headed, than yours; and therefore by your own argument, more sure and certain.

*{with us are}* Some think that he means, that himself and Bildad were ancienter than Job’s father, though Zophar might be younger than Job, as seems by Job’s answer to him, Chap. 12. 12. And they read it, *There is a gray-headed man among us*, (meaning himself) *and a very aged man*, (meaning Bildad) So runs the Chaldee paraphrase. But that is doubtful, as appears by the notes there. And the Chaldee paraphrase makes Zophar elder than Job’s father. It may be meant simply. There are ancienter men than your father now alive, and not in other countries only, but in ours, of whom we have learned what we speak, and find them to be of our mind. And these should know more than you by your own argument.

*{both the gray-headed, and very aged men}* For these words, see the notes on Chap. 12. 12. where both these words are expounded.

*{much elder then}* Heb. *more in dayes then*. See on Chap. 8. 2.

*{thy father}* The word אב in the original comes from אבה, to desire, or to delight in a thing, because most men desire children, and parents take more pleasure in them, than in all their possessions, Gen. 15. 2. & 30. 1. 1 Sam. 1. 8, 11. The word signifies, 1. An immediate father, Ruth 4. 17. 2. Ancestors, Gen. 31. 3. 3. Rulers in a commonwealth or family, 2 King. 5. 13. 4. Teachers, 2 King. 2. 12. 5. A reliever; for rulers and teachers, and relievers, take care of others, as parents do of children, Chap. 29. 16. 6. The first inventor of anything, Gen. 4. 20. For children went not abroad then to learn, but were taught of their fathers, and continued ordinarily in the same trade, Gen. 47. 3. Here it is taken in the first sense, for Job’s immediate father. For no doubt but Job had ancestors as old as they.

**V. 11. ***{Are the consolations of God small with thee? is there any secret thing with thee?}* Here Job’s friends go on in an ironical way to deride and vilify Job, as one that thought too highly of himself, and too meanly of them, and that in this verse, and the two verses following. He proves it by an effect of his pride, in the beginning of this verse, contempt of divine consolation. Which is set out by the cause of it, in the end of this verse, and in the 12. verse, to wit, blindness of mind, and impotency arising either out of too much confidence in himself, or too deep sense of his miseries. And this charge is made good from the effect of it, ver. 13. angry words uttered against God. The sum of the verse is, as if Job should have said, These comforts which we bring you, as it were from God himself, they are too slender belike, and not for your liking: doubtless there lies hidden in you some greater and more profound wisdom, than every man knows.

*{Are}* It seems they are slighted by you; but they should not be so; and therefore we marvel at your strange carriage. See the like question of admiration, and not of denial, Chap. 14. 14. Look the notes there.

*{the consolations of God}* Some understand it of those comforts Job had had from God before in the time of his prosperity, as if Eliphaz went about to accuse Job of forgetfulness of God’s former proceedings with him, the remembrance whereof might have been a great comfort to him now in his troubles. But this was far from Eliphaz his scope, who endeavored not to comfort Job, but to discourage him. It is rather to be understood of those comfortable promises that Eliphaz, and his companions, had given to Job in their former dispute with him, promising him from God much future prosperity, if he did repent. Others read it, *the consolations of these men*. Meaning the two last that had spoken, and in modesty saying nothing of himself. So he blames him for despising their counsels, and in a kind of arrogancy calls them, *The consolations of God*. Men are easily in love with, and think highly of their own projects. For אל, *God*, see on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{small with thee}* Heb. *too little for thee*. Do you slight them, as things beneath you? Do you expect greater promises from God to draw you to repentance, than we have given you? It seems you do, because you are so slow in hearkening to us.

*{is there any secret thing with thee}* Are you by some secret revelation better acquainted with God’s mind than we? It seems you think so, and therefore despise our counsels. Eliphaz speaks this with a kind of contempt. See the like speech, 1 King. 22. 24. Others read it, *And the word gently spoken to thee*. So it is translated, 1 Sam. 18. 5. But the former agrees best with the scope of the place. For the word דבר, *a thing*, see on verse 3. of this chapter.

**V. 12. ***{Why doth thine heart carry thee away? and what do thine eyes wink at?}* Tell me I pray you what the reason is, that you are thus strangely transported and carried out of yourself, beyond all bounds of reason and modesty, to lift up yourself against God, and mock us?

*{Why}* So this word מה is translated, Chap. 7. 21. But ordinarily it signifies *What*; as ver. 9. of this chapter.

*{Doth thine heart}* The word לב is taken, 1. For that fleshly member, which we call by that name, 2 Sam. 18. 14. 2. For the middle of anything, because the heart lies in the middle of the body, Jon. 2. 3. 3. For the soul itself, which brings life, and resides first and last in the heart, 1 Sam. 25. 5. 4. For the thought of the heart, Chap. 1. 5. 2 King. 5. 26. 5. For the affection, 2 King. 10. 15. 6. For knowledge or understanding, Prov. 15. 32. Here it is taken in the fourth sense, for the thought of the heart.

*{carry thee away}* לקח signifies, 1. To receive a thing given, Deut. 16. 19. 2. To take away a thing by violence, Chap. 9. 12. 2 Sam. 12. 30. 3. To take a thing bought, 2 Sam. 4. 6. 4. To receive instruction, or to learn, Chap. 22. 22. Prov. 1. 3. 5. To allure or take one with words, Prov. 6. 25. & 11. 30. Here it is taken in the second sense, for a violent transportation, or carrying a man out of himself.

*{and what}* Or, *why*; as before in this verse.

*{do thine eyes wink at}* The Hebrew word here used, is not found elsewhere in Scripture. But the phrase of winking with the eyes, is used to express a gesture of mocking, Psal. 35. 19. Prov. 6. 13. & 10. 10. It may be that Job was forced by reason of his pain and sores to wink in his own speeches or his friends; and this they uncharitably interpret, as if he did it in contempt of God or them, as the words foregoing in this verse, and the words following in the next verse do import.

**V. 13. ***{That thou turnest thy spirit against God, and lettest such words go out of thy mouth?}* This verse is an explanation of the former; for here he shows to what his heart did carry him away so violently, to wit, to speak such bitter words against God and his providence, as he had formerly uttered.

*{That}* This particle has many other significations, but is thus translated, 2 King. 8. 13.

*{thou turnest}* The Hebrew word השיב in this conjugation signifies diversely: 1. When it is spoken of a thing, it signifies restoring it to his place, or bringing it back again, Gen. 14. 16. 2. When of words, it signifies answering, or rendering of words back again, Gen. 37. 14. 3. When of good or evil, it is to repay, reward, or punish, Gen. 50. 15. 4. When of anger, it imports restraining of it, or reconciliation, Numb. 25. 11. 5. When of the face, it signifies rejecting, or denying his request, 1 King. 2. 16. *Deny me not.* Heb. *turn not away my face*. See in the margin there. 6. When of the heart, it intends calling a thing to mind, or thinking of it, Isa. 46. 8. 7. When of the soul, it signifies to bring it back out of sin, or sorrow, to convert, or comfort it, Psal. 19. 7. Lam. 1. 16. 8. When of an action, it signifies turning out of a way we were in before, or come to some other; as a traveler turns his feet out of one path into another, Dan. 11. 18, 19. So here Job is taxed for leaving that patient and submissive way he was in. Chap. 1. and going on in that bitter way of complaining against God and his proceedings, which he began, Chap. 3.

*{thy spirit}* See on verse 2. Here it is taken for the soul. You speak as earnestly against God, as if your heart were clean turned against him.

*{against}* Or, *to God*. You take it off from men, as if you had nothing to say to them, but all against God. Yet אל is translated *Against*, 2 Chron. 11. 4. And so it may well be here.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{and lettest such words}* The word *such*, is not in the original, but well added, to express the sense. For it is not a fault to speak, but to speak amiss. What manner of words these were, appears by Eliphaz his answer to them in the next verse, to wit, impatient words, formerly uttered by Job against God’s proceedings with him. For *words*, see on verse 3.

*{go out}* Heb. *makest to go out*. The like word sometimes signifies a permission in this conjugation, as Psal. 119. 175. But Job did more than suffer such words to go out of his mouth; for he willingly spoke them. At least is so charged by Eliphaz. For the word *go out*, יצא, It signifies, 1. Going out of one place to another, Gen. 8. 7. 2. It is used of the birth of living creatures, when they go out of them into the world, 2 King. 20. 18. 3. It is spoken of things growing out of the earth, Deut. 14. 22. 4. Of the rising of the sun, Gen. 19. 23. 5. Of speech, which comes out of the mouth, and is spread and scattered abroad, Esth. 1. 17. 6. Of some ecstasy of the soul, when through admiration, grief, or fear, it is as it were gone out of itself, Gen. 42. 28. 7. Of laying out in expenses, 2 King. 12. 12. 8. Of going out armed for war, Gen. 14. 8. 9. Of escaping by lot, and so going out of danger, 1 Sam. 14. 41. Here it is used in the fifth sense, and Job is accused of speaking evil words against God.

*{of thy mouth}* See on verse 5. Here it is used for the mouth itself, the instrument of speaking.

**V. 14. ***{What is man that he should be clean? and he which is born of a woman, that he should be righteous?}* Before he had reproved Job, now he falls to the work to prove that he had spoken vain words, as he had charged him, verse 3. He answers Job’s words of justification of himself, Chap. 13. 18, 19, 23. His proofs are taken from the corruption of man’s nature, which might be true, though Job were not a wicked man. And Eliphaz shows himself an empty and troublesome disputer, because he uses the same arguments here, verse 14, 15, 16. that he did, Chap. 4. verse 17, 18, 19. The sum of the verse is, that Job being born in sin as other men, cannot be innocent before God.

*{What is}* Spoken with admiration, as in the like phrase is used, Psal. 8. 4. with what face can you pronounce yourself righteous, being born as sinful as other men? No man is of that account with God, that he can clear himself, Chap. 14. 4. 1 King. 8. 46. 2 Chr. 6. 36. Psal. 14. 3. Prov. 20. 9. 1 Joh. 1. 8.

*{man}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{that he should be clean}* See on Chap. 11. 4. to which place it is likely Eliphaz here alludes. See the like charged on him by Elihu, Chapter 33. 8, 9.

*{that is born of a woman}* Chap. 14. 1.

*{that he should be righteous}* Or, *justified*; as Chap. 4. 17.

**V. 15. ***{Behold he putteth no trust in his Saints; yea the heavens are not clean in his sight.}* Eliphaz proves by an argument from the greater to the less, that Job cannot be clean before God, whose knowledge is so exact, that he can find obscurity in the saints and angels, at least in respect of his own brightness and purity.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{he putteth no trust}* See on Chap. 4. 18.

*{in his Saints}* In the saints in heaven, or the holy angels. See on Chap. 4. 18. See Matth. 18. 10. & 24. 36.

*{the heavens}* See on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. Some understand it of the visible heavens. Others of the angels that dwell in the highest heavens. And so it agrees well with the beginning of this verse, and with Chap. 4. 18. So it is put for God himself, Dan. 4. 26. who is said to be in heaven, Matth. 6. 9. to have his throne there, Isa. 66. 1. to be the King of heaven, Dan. 4. 37.

*{are not clean}* If it be understood of the visible heavens, then the meaning is, they are not all alike clear and bright, but have in them stars, and some thicker parts or clouds, which are as so many spots in them. If, of the angels of heaven, see how they are unclean, in the notes on Chap. 4. 18.

*{in his sight}* Heb. *in his eyes*. So Prov. 1. 17. for the eye is the instrument of sight. As if he had said, You O Job, think yourself clean, because men can find no fault with you: but God’s eyes are clearer than men’s. He can find out your secret sins. Others understand it, in comparison of God. Which though it be true, yet the phrase in the original will hardly bear it.

**V. 16. ***{How much more abominable and filthy is man, which drinketh iniquity like water?}* The argument was in the former verse taken from saints and angels. The conclusion is in this verse brought down to men, who are in a far worse condition.

*{How much more}* Or, *How much lesse*. According as it is knit to the former or latter part of the verse. See the same on chap. 4. 19.

*{abominable}* The word signifies a thing that men cannot endure to look upon, no to have in their presence, Exod. 8. 26. And that may well be set in opposition to the beautiful heavens, which though they be not clean in God’s sight, verse 15. yet are delightful to men’s eyes. So is man abominable in God’s eyes, and like unto meat, which the stomach cannot endure, Chap. 33. 20.

*{filthy}* Noisome, stinking, like meat putrefied for want of salt, displeasing to the palate, and nose. So are men in their natural condition filthy in God’s account, Psal. 14. 3. and 53. 3.

*{is man}* For the word, see on chap. 1. 1. It is like that he gives Job a private nip here. You have been a man of great note, yet God shows by these plagues laid upon you, that you are abominable in his sight.

*{which drinketh iniquity like water}* Who is naturally as greedy to commit sin, as a thirsty man is to drink water. It seems Elihu understood this to be spoken of Job, and charges him with it in particular, Chap. 34. 7. Prov. 26. 6. A proverbial speech to signify plenty and abundance of sin in man; who as he was born in sin, so is as it were bred and brought up in it. For the word *Drinking*, see on chap. 6. 4. For the word *iniquity*, see on Chap. 13. 7. For the word *water*, see on chap. 14. 7.

**V. 17. ***{I will shew thee, hear me, and that which I have seen, I will declare}* Eliphaz being now to begin a new matter, to wit, to prove, that God afflicts none, but wicked men so grievously, as he afflicted Job, he begins with a new preface, to get attention. And that in the 17, 18, and 19. verses. And in some high terms, that Job might not contemn what he had to say, as a thing full of uncertainty. He tells him therefore, that he need not think scorn to be taught by him, for he would produce nothing, but what he had either seen with his own eyes, or received from wise men, and those no ordinary ones neither, but such as were very eminent for power, and justice. And thus he seeks to raise in Job an expectation of no mean things, but of great matters from him, not received from contemptible men, but wise observations delivered by famous ones, who for their wisdom had whole provinces committed to their charge to be governed by them. So he boasted before of his night vision, Chap. 4. 12.

*{I will shew thee}* I will set that before you, and make it plain to you, which yet you know not. Now what it was, that he should show him, appears by the following discourse. I will plainly demonstrate to you, that what I, and my fellows have said before, that only wicked men are thus sharply handled, is true. This argument Eliphaz was upon before, Chap. 4. 7. and Bildad, Chap. 8. 13.

*{hear me}* For the various significations of the Hebrew word, and the right use of it here, see on Chap. 3. 17.

*{and that which I have seen}* Which I have gathered in my time, by observing God’s judgments on ungodly men. And therefore what I shall speak is a certain truth, and worthy of credit, as coming from an eyewitness, 1 Joh. 1. 1.

*{I will declare}* The word signifies sometimes to number, as Gen. 15. 5. And this we also call *telling*, in English. But here it signifies, to tell a thing by declaring of it: as also Chap. 12. 8. Some read the latter end of the verse thus, *For this have I seen, therefore I will declare it*. I may be confident, I hope, in declaring what I have seen with my own eyes. This reading gives a greater emphasis to Eliphaz his words. And for the confirmation of it we shall find, that the Hebrew particle, ו is translated *for*, and does not barely knit things together, but gives a reason of what went before, Chap. 11. 4. And it is also translated *therefore*, and infers a thing out of a former ground, Lev. 18. 30.

**V. 18. ***{Which wise men have told from their fathers, and have not hid it.}* The whole verse may be translated thus, *And which wise men have told, and have not hid, having received it from their fathers.* For so the words stand in the original, and are more plain. What himself had observed, he further confirms by the testimony of wise men, and brings in their parents also for witnesses of the truth of it. And lest Job should slight his authority, he backs it with a second argument, taken from the consent and harmony of former ages. So he reasoned, ver. 10. and Bildad goes the same way, Chap. 8. 8.

*{Which}* Or, *which things*. I say nothing, but what was said long since.

*{wise men}* Men that had gotten much knowledge in God’s proceedings, by study and observation.

*{have told}* Told me, or others, from whom I had it. It comes from a word that signifies before, or in presence. They have spoken so plainly and fully, as if they had showed us the things acted before our eyes.

*{from their fathers}* For the various use of the word in Scripture, see on verse 10. It is here taken for ancestors, from whom, by tradition, they had taken it, and communicated it to their children, Psal. 78. 3, 4, 5, 6. Others understand it from the time of their fathers. Thus has been the constant received truth. Both interpretations plead from antiquity.

*{and have not hid it}* From their posterity, but faithfully communicated it to them, as their fathers had done to them, that it might not perish. For it is likely, that no Scripture was written in Job’s time, but God appeared in some eminent men, and revealed his will to them, and they taught it faithfully to their posterity. But now he has written his mind fully in his word, and therefore there is no need of traditions. This doubling the phrase, in other words, is for the greater certainty of the truth of it, Joh. 3. 20. See on Chap. 14. 10, 11. The word כחד signifies to hide a thing, so as it may not be seen, or heard, or appear, 2 Sam. 18. 13. 2. To be cut off: for things cut off can appear no more, Exod. 9. 15. Here it is taken in the first sense, for hiding. They revealed it, that it might not be lost by silence.

**V. 19. ***{Unto whom alone the earth was given, and no stranger passed among them}* Here he gives Job a ground to give credit to what he should say, if not for his sake, yet for those worthy men’s, from whom these excellent instructions came, being men, not driven up and down, as wicked and ungodly men are, by God’s judgments, but men seated quietly in their habitations, and living plentifully, yea, coming to authority in their country, and by God’s blessing, keeping out all enemies, and so being examples of the continued prosperity of godly men, which they held out in words, and therefore worthy to be believed.

*{Unto whom}* Heb. *To them*. It may be understood, either of the wise men mentioned in the former verse, or of their fathers, or of both: and that last makes the argument the stronger, as flowing from consent of judgment, and continued prosperity of those men for divers generations.

*{alone}* Heb. *by themselves*. No others did dwell among them. They were a distinct people singularly blessed by God, Num. 23. 9.

*{the earth}* See on Chap. 9. 24. Or, *the land*, to wit, that land wherein they lived.

*{was given}* Given by God for an inheritance to them, and their posterity to live in, and so they did for many generations. A great blessing of God. So the word is used, Gen. 17. 8. Or committed to them, for their wisdom and justice, by the people to rule, and so the argument is the stronger. They were no mean men, but great princes of great experience. Thus the word is used, Psal. 2. 8, 9, 10. & 1 King. 11. 11. See on Chap. 9. 24. For the various significations of the word נתן. See on Chap. 14. 4. on the word, *Bring*.

*{and no stranger}* No man of another nation to molest them, or no man of another family to take away their authority.

*{passed}* As an enemy in a hostile way to go up and down, in a warlike manner, to slay them or spoil their country, Nah. 1. 15. The Heb. word עבר signifies; 1. To pass through, as Gen. 15. 17. 2. To die. For as men by birth come into the world, so by death they pass out of it, Psal. 37. 36. 3. To pass by, or forgive a fault, Mic. 7. 18. 4. To pass through a land to punish it, Amos 5. 17. 5. To pass beyond, or violate an oath or covenant, Josh. 7. 11. 6. To pass over, or overcome one, Jer. 23. 9. Here it may be taken in the fourth sense, for passing through the country to hurt it. No doubt they might pass through peaceably, and for traffic.

*{among them}* Heb. *in the midst of them*. The meaning is, either that the people, by God’s blessing lived so quietly, that no man molested them; or else, that the governors guided them so wisely, that no enemy dared meddle with them to take away their government, or oppress their people.

**V. 20. ***{The wicked man travaileth with pain all his days, and the number of years is hidden to the oppressor}* Now he shows what were those wise sentences, so much commended by him, which he received from those famous men, and at what he principally aimed. He sets out to the end of the chapter, the curse and destruction of wicked men, how that even in prosperity they live always miserably, expecting a change, and what they fear comes upon them, and then they despair having no hope of escaping, but it is far otherwise with godly men. The same things he had spoken before, Chap. 4. & 5. but here he delivers them with great variety of phrases and figures which is very delightful. He sets out the misery of ungodly men by their pains, in this verse, their fears, ver. 21, 22. their wants, ver. 23. their troubles, verse 24. the causes of all these miseries, ver. 25, 26, 27. and last of all, by their certain destruction, from ver. 28. to the end of the chapter. All or most of these things, he conceives had befallen Job, because he confessed such things were come upon him, Chap. 17. 13, 14. & 3. 25, 26. which things are threatened by God, against ungodly men, Levit. 26. 36. Deut. 28. 65. and therefore Job must needs be a wicked man by his own concessions. As if Eliphaz had said in this verse, O Job, you feel that in yourself, which I have told you before, and now tell you again, that a wicked man is most miserable. There is not one day goes over his head, in which he does not marvelously torment himself in all that term of years, that God permits him to live here on earth.

*{the wicked man}* See on Chap. 3. 17.

*{travaileth with pain}* Fills himself with care, and sorrows and pains, like a woman in travail; for so much the word imports, Psal. 48. 6. Others read it, *Kills himself*, or *thrusts himself through*. Suitable to the apostle’s expression, 1 Tim. 6. verse 10.

*{all his days}* As long as he lives. He will never give himself any rest. For the word *Day* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{and the number of years}* The time how long he shall live upon the face of the earth. As the wicked man’s life is full of pain, and trouble, so he knows not how soon his life may be taken away.

*{is hidden}* Is kept so close from him, that he can no more tell the day of his death, than men can see things that are hidden.

*{to the oppressor}* Or, *To the mighty one*. It comes from a word that signifies to fear, and notes out such men, as by reason of their power and cruelty, make others afraid of them. Others read the latter end of the verse thus, *And in the number of years, which is hidden to the oppressor.* And then it is the same in sense with the former, only it is delivered in other words, for emphasis’ sake. It is certain, he shall have trouble all his life long. See on Chap. 14. 10, 11. Others read it thus, *And soon numbred years, are stored up for the Tyrant.* That is, God in his secret decree, has appointed him but a short time to live. The number of years then is the same the years of number, Chap. 16. 22. and men of number, Gen. 34. 30. That is, few, that may be quickly numbered. So may the days of tyrants, who are soon cut off for their cruelty.

**V. 21. ***{A dreadful sound is in his ear; in prosperity the destroyer shall come upon him}* In this verse, he sets out the unquiet condition of ungodly men; both in regard of inward fears, and also of outward dangers. The sum is, His ears ring always with horrible, and fearful sounds; and when he shall think himself to be in greatest safety, even then shall the enemy come upon him, utterly to destroy him.

*{a dreadful sound: Heb. a sound of fears, or terrors}* That is, a most terrible noise. He thinks he hears terrible voices, telling him (and that not untruly in the end) that the enemy, or danger is near, which threatens sudden destruction to him every moment. And he says of terrors in the plural number; that is of many terrors at once; or one coming after another, presently without intermission, and giving him no rest at all. It is likely he alludes to those sad messages, that came so thick on Job in the end of the first chapter. The name of a noise carries terror with it, telling as it were, that danger is at hand, Jer. 4. 19. Nah. 3. 2. The word קול, signifies; 1. A noise, or sound in general, though not articulate, as Exod. 9. 28. Ezek. 1. 24. 2. A voice of words, distinctly spoken, as Gen. 21. 12. Here it is taken in the first sense, for some great noise, causing fear. The word פחד, signifies; 1. The act of fear, Psal. 31. 11. 2. The person feared, Gen. 31. 53. 3. The thing, or evil feared, Prov. 3. 25. Here it is taken in the third sense, for dangers, that make men afraid: so it is also taken, Prov. 1. 26, 27.

*{is in his ears}* Or, *sounds in his eares*; which is more elegant. The verb is understood in the original. He means, that his fear makes him perpetually to think of dangers, as if the sound of them were within his ears, so as it could never be gotten out. The word אזן *ear*, in the original signifies; 1. The instrument of hearing, Levit. 8. 23. 2. The act of hearing, Deut. 31. 11. So here. He thinks he always hears such a noise.

*{in prosperity}* Heb. *in peace*. So the word properly signifies; and because peace brings prosperity, it is put for that also. He shall be destroyed, when he is at highest, Dan. 4. 30, 31.

*{the destroyer}* If he have any time of quietness granted him, yet then shall the destroyer come unexpectedly to overthrow him utterly, 1 Thess. 5. 3.

*{shall come upon him}* The Hebrew word בוא signifies; 1. To come to a place, Gen. 43. 21. 2. When it is spoken of the sun, it signifies the setting of it, Gen. 28. 11. 3. When of a man’s carriage to a woman, it is a modest expression of lying with her, Psal. 51. in the title. 4. When of days, it notes out old age, Gen. 24. 1. 5. When of words, it signifies the fulfilling of them, 1 Sam. 9. 6. 6. When of corn, it intimates the gathering of it, Hag. 1. 6. 7. When of a congregation or society of men, it notes admission into it, as a member to enjoy all the privileges of it, Deut. 23. 3. 8. When of a covenant, it signifies making of a covenant, or accepting of it, Jer. 34. 10. 9. When with application to fathers, it signifies dying, Gen. 15. 15. 10. When of a thing without life, it notes the coming to pass of a thing, Chap. 1. 19. 11. When it is joined with going out, it signifies administering an office in the commonwealth, church, or family, Numb. 27. 17. Here it is taken in the first sense, for coming into his habitation, with a purpose to destroy him.

**V. 22. ***{He believeth not that he shall return out of darkness, and he is waited for of the sword}* If the wicked man fall into adversity, he has no hope at all to be delivered, but despairs, looking for nothing but death, as if he saw a sword always hanging over his head. He is persuaded his troubles will bring him to death. This also reflects upon Job’s speeches, Chap. 10. 20, 21, 22.

*{He believeth not}* The word האמין signifies; 1. to give credit to a thing, or believe it to be true, Psal. 116. 10. 2. to put trust in one, or confidence, as Chap. 4. 18. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{that he shall return}* Heb. *to return*. To wit, out of his misery to his former prosperity. For the various significations of the word, see on Chap. 14. 13.

*{out of darkness}* The word signifies darkness literally, but here, as in other places, it is put for calamity, which takes away the light and delight of comforts.

*{and he is waited for}* He is in danger of the sword, as if it had understanding, and did watch for him continually, to have an opportunity to kill him. Thus he lives in continual fear. Or, *looked upon*. For those things we wait for, we think often of, and look on them steadfastly, if they come in our sight. They take our eyes off from other objects, Psal. 37. 32. Or, *but that he is waited for*: He is always in fear of death.

*{of the sword}* Or, *by the sword*. As this particle is translated, Jer. 33. 4. Or, *to be delivered to the sword*. One verb included in another: waited for to the sword: that is, waited for to be delivered to the sword. See the like v. 8. The word חרב, 1. signifies the instrument of killing, the sword, which destroys many, Psal. 89. 43. 2. for murder, which is often done by the sword, Isai. 65. 12. If it be translated, Of the sword, then it is taken in the first sense: If, To the sword, then in the second. Some read the latter part thus, *But that he is waited for of the sword*; that is, he is so far from believing that he shall escape, that he certainly persuades himself that he shall be slain. For ו, But, see on Chap. 14. 10.

**V. 23. ***{He wandereth abroad for bread, saying, Where is it? he knoweth that the day of darkness is ready at his hand}* When God begins to afflict him, and take away his estate, he does go abroad, to seek for things needful to preserve his life, but he stands always in fear of death.

*{He wandereth abroad}* It may signify fear. He goes swiftly from place to place, like a banished man, and dares tarry nowhere, lest he should be slain, Gen. 4. 14. Isai. 16. 2. Prov. 27. 8. Or, it may signify extreme poverty. He that was rich before, now goes up and down like a beggar to seek for food, Psal. 37. 25. & 59. 15. & 109. 10.

*{for bread}* The Hebrew word לחם signifies; 1. Food, in general, or meat, 1 King. 5. 9. 2. Bread, which is man’s common and chief food, Gen. 25. 34. 3. Corn, of which bread is made, Gen. 41. 54. 4. For fruit of trees, which is food for living creatures, Jer. 11. 19. Here it is taken in the first sense, for food: Yet it may be understood of the meanest food, sought for by him, with great labor and travel. He would be glad of a piece of bread, that might but sustain life, if he can get it, 1 Sam. 2. 5, 36.

*{saying, Where is it?}* Or, *to seek where it is*. One verb included in another, as Isai. 38. 14, 17, 21. He leaves no way untried, that so he may get some. This shows his great necessity.

*{he knoweth}* He has a certain persuasion of it in his heart. It is a great part of the punishment of wicked men, that their mind forebodes them evil. He perceives inevitable misery coming upon him.

*{that the day of darkness}* For the word *day*, see on Chap. 14. 14. here it is put for the time. For *darkness*, see on ver. 22. of this chapter. Yet here it is not put for trouble, as there, but rather for death, which brings a man to the grave, the house of darkness, Chap. 10. 21, 22. Eccl. 6. 4. & 11. 8. Psal. 143. 3. He was already in trouble, as appears in the beginning of the verse, that was upon him, and therefore could not be ready for him. But he persuaded himself, that he should never be rid of his trouble till he died, and thought that would quickly come to pass.

*{is ready at his hand}* Very near. As sure, as if he were already destroyed. So he thinks that God, who has already made him poor, will soon kill him. Death is imminent, he expects it every moment, Chap. 18. 12. The Hebrew word, יד: 1. Signifies that member of the body, so called, Jer. 2. 37. 2. Strength, or power, Deut. 32. 36. 3. Counsel, or advice, 2 Sam. 14. 19. 4. A stroke, or plague, Chap. 13. 21. 5. A place, Deut. 23. 12. 6. Power, or possession, Chap. 2. 6. 7. Actions, which are usually done with the hands, Psal. 24. 4. 8. An instrument; as the hand is of many actions, Psal. 17. 14. 9. Bounty, 1 King. 10. 13. for we give with the hand. 10. Labor, Levit. 25. 47. Here it may be taken in the fifth sense, for a place, and ready at hand, is beside him, or near to him; as 1 Sam. 19. 3. 2 Sam. 14. 30. He is in great danger of death, Chap. 13. 14. He lives always in fear of it, Deut. 28. 66.

**V. 24. ***{Trouble and anguish shall make him afraid; they shall prevail against him, as a King ready to the battle}* He goes on to describe the misery, that God will bring upon wicked men, when he takes them to task for their sins.

*{Trouble}* It comes from a word, that signifies to keep in, or straiten a man, or to besiege a city, so that men have not room enough to breathe, or to get things necessary. It imports any great trouble or grief, whereby man’s heart is straitened, kept in and oppressed, so that he knows not how to get out of it.

*{and anguish}* It is derived from a word, that signifies cleaving fast to one, like a disease that cannot be parted from the body, that pains a man sore. Both words argue the greatness, and unavoidableness of their misery, Prov. 1. 27.

*{shall make him afraid}* The troubles he feels, shall make him fear more and greater, coming on him.

*{they shall prevail against him}* They shall not only scare him, but also utterly overcome him. See on Chap. 14. 20.

*{as a king ready to the battle}* As a king, having a great army about him, ready to storm a city, puts it into a great affright, and suddenly destroys it, so shall their troubles do to them, Isai. 29. 3. The word is taken from another word, that signifies a ball, Isai. 22. 18. For soldiers in a siege lie round about a city, that none may go in or out, to compel the place to yield to them.

**V. 25. ***{For he stretcheth out his hand against God, and strengtheneth himself against the Almighty}* Now he comes to set out the judgments, and punishments, wherewith God will afflict and destroy ungodly men, and sets the causes of these judgments before, that all men might know that God’s vengeance on them was most just. And both the causes and judgments are set out with excellent metaphors and similitudes. In this verse and the next, there is as it were a pitched battle betwixt the wicked man and God.

*{For}* Some would have the causes of the troubles mentioned before, to be here set down, why the wicked man is in so much danger of troubles, ere they come; and despairs of help when they come on him, namely, opposing himself against God, who can destroy him in despite of all helpers, Chap. 9. 13. and from whose power no man can deliver him, Psal. 50. 22. Hos. 5. 14. Others read it, *Because*. And refer it to the words following, as the cause of God’s opposing him, verse 26. So כי, is translated, Chap. 11. 16.

*{he stretcheth out}* Or, *he hath stretched out*. For he speaks of his former proud carriage in time of his prosperity, as ver. 27. not of his present carriage now in his adversity, as verse 23, 24.

*{his hand}* For this word, see on ver. 23. For the phrase of stretching out the hand, see on Chap. 11. 13. yet the word is not used here for a gesture of prayer, as there; but for resisting or smiting, as an enemy stretches out his hand in battle to destroy his enemy; as Zeph. 1. 4. So would the wicked man destroy God, if he could. Or it may be taken for sinning against God with a stretched out, or high hand, or with all his power, Lev. 26. 21. Num. 15. 30.

*{against God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{and strengtheneth himself}* Like a strong warrior he bends all his strength against God, whom no man can resist, and who will overthrow all his enemies. It may be read, *And hath strengthened himself*; to wit, before, in his prosperity.

*{against the Almighty}* Who can do all things, is sufficient for himself, needs no helps, nor weapons, nor forts, but is of himself invincible. See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

**V. 26. ***{He runneth upon him, even on his neck, upon the thick bosses of his bucklers.}* Seeing they use all their strength against God, God also will set upon them, like a furious enemy, that regards neither their strength, nor armor, but runs fearlessly upon them, and throws them down to the ground.

*{He}* Some interpret it of the wicked man, who in the former verse was said to strengthen himself against God; and in this verse they conceive, that his fearlessness and impudence is set down, so that nothing can scare him from sinning against God. But it is rather to be understood of God’s proceeding against the wicked man in judgment, as he did against him in sin, verse 25. *He*, may have reference to either; for both are mentioned in the former verse.

*{runneth upon him}* A similitude from a valiant soldier, who stays not till his enemy sets on him, but runs apace, and sets fiercely on him, as soon as he sets eye upon him, 1 Sam. 17. 48. Dan. 8. 6.

*{even on his neck}* He stands not afar off, but comes close to him, and takes him about the neck to cast him down; or strangle him. Others read it, *with his neck*. That is, with a stretched out neck, to show his forwardness to fall upon him. So wool is put for white wool, Isa. 1. 18. A yoke on the neck, signifies slavery, in Scripture phrase, Gen. 27. 40. Falling on the neck, intimates love, Gen. 45. 14. Speaking with a stiff neck, shows arrogancy, Psal. 75. 5. Hardening the neck, sets out impenitency, Jer. 19. 15. But running on the neck, here intimates a violent, and sudden destruction of the wicked man.

*{upon the thick}* His strong armor, that by reason of the thickness thereof, he thinks can keep out bullets and darts. The word is used sometimes to set out a cloud, which is the thickest part of the sky, 1 King. 18. 44. Sometimes to set out the thick woods, Jer. 4. 29. Sometimes the clay, or thick earth; as 2 Chr. 4. 17.

*{bosses}* The word גב signifies a thing that is higher than the rest. As 1. A brothel, or building higher than others, Ezek. 16. 24. 2. The back of beasts, which uses to be highest, Psal. 129. 3. 3. The eyebrows, that are above the eye, Lev. 14. 9. 4. The upper part of the wheel, 1 King. 7. 33. 5. For the body of man, which goes upright, and is higher than the beasts, Job 13. 12. 6. For the bosses and highest parts of a buckler, as here. Others read it, *the backs*, in the second sense, because bucklers were wont to be made of the backs of beasts doubled.

*{of his buckler}* מגן comes from a word that signifies to protect, or defend. And signifies, First, A buckler, or shield, wherewith the body is defended from wounds in battle, 2 Sam. 1. 21. Secondly, By a metaphor it signifies protection itself from any dangers, Gen. 15. 1. 3. Kings and rulers, who are the protectors of the people, Hos. 4. 18. Here it is taken in the second sense. And the meaning is, that though the wicked man have much wealth and strength more than others, which he trusts to, as a soldier to a thick buckler with high bosses on it, yet God will set upon him, and rout him for all that. Others read it, *with the thick bosses of his buckler*. God will send exquisite and high judgments upon him.

**V. 27. ***{Because he covereth his face with his fatness, and maketh collops of fat on his flanks}* Though he live plentifully and deliciously for a time, yet he shall come to poverty; as in the following verses is expressed. He seems tacitly to accuse Job and his children, as pampering themselves in their prosperity, and so bringing hasty ruin upon themselves. Peradventure he alludes to Job’s children feasting one another by turns, Chap. 1. 4.

*{Because}* Their glutting themselves, is the cause of their ensuing ruin. Or, *Though*: Although he did live in a palace, and fared daintily, yet he shall come to extreme poverty. So the word is used, Psal. 44. 19.

*{he covereth}* Not that his face is hidden, but it is all over fat, as a thing covered is hid all over. Nothing but fat can be seen in it. No part of it is lean.

*{his face}* See on Chap. 14. 20. The face only is named, because that lies open to all men’s view, when the fat of the body is covered with the apparel.

*{with his fatness}* The word literally signifies the fat part of the body of man or beast, as Deut. 32. 14. metaphorically it is put for the excellentest of anything, as the fat of the land, that is the best fruits of the earth, Gen. 44. 18. Some understand this phrase of covering his face with fatness, literally, of fatting himself up with the plentiful, and superfluous use of God’s creatures given to him in abundance, Chap. 21. 23. Psal. 17. 10. & 73. 7. Others understand it metaphorically of being proud of his prosperity; as Deut. 32. 15. Psal. 78. 31. Jer. 46. 21.

*{and maketh}* See on Chap. 14. 9.

*{collops of fat}* So much that one may as it were take it up by handfuls.

*{on his flanks}* As Lev. 3. 4. Or, *Loines*; as Psal. 38. 7. To intimate a full delighting of himself in the creatures, and fattening himself in every part, like a fat ox, or abundance of pride, by reason of abundance of wealth.

**V. 28. ***{And he dwelleth in desolate cities, and in houses which no man inhabiteth, which are ready to become heaps.}* Some understand it of the continuance of his prosperity. He grew so rich, that he was able to build famous palaces in places uninhabited before, or decayed. See on Chap. 3. 14. Others conceive it to be the beginning of the setting out of his poverty. He is glad now to dwell in desert places, being driven out of his stately palaces.

*{And}* Or, *Yet*. So it is translated, Psal. 119. 109, 110. He has been very rich, but now is come to extreme poverty.

*{he dwelleth}* Makes his constant abiding in such places.

*{in desolate}* The word כחד 1. Signifies *Hidden*, as Psal. 69. 5. 2. Cut off, or destroyed, or desolate, Exod. 9. 15. For cities destroyed by the enemy lie hid as it were, from men’s sight. Some understand it of places ruined by his oppression.

*{cities}* עיר comes from a word, that in the original signifies to awake or stir up, because it is as it were awaked or stirred up out of the dust, as a man out of sleep. It is put, 1. For the city itself, Gen. 19. 4. 2. For the inhabitants of it, Mic. 6. 9. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{and in houses}* בית comes from בנה *to build*. Houses grow not like trees, but must be made by men’s labor. It is put, 1. For the habitations of men, Prov. 1. 13. 2. For the nests of birds, which are their houses or dwelling places, Psal. 84. 3. 3. For the household, family, nation, or posterity, as the house of Jacob, the house of Judah, the house of Israel. Here it is used in the first sense, for their dwelling places.

*{which no man inhabiteth}* Unfit for dwelling, and ready to fall down on men’s heads.

*{which are ready to become heaps}* Which are very ruinous, and within a little time will fall to the ground, and the stones thereof lie on heaps. Such as are not fit for a man to dwell in. The word גל, signifies, 1. An heap of stones, Gen. 31. 46. 2. An heap of water, or a wave, Psal. 65. 7. 3. A fountain, which has abundance of water in it, as an heap has plenty of stones, Cant. 4. 12. In this place it is taken in the first sense, for an heap of stones. See on Chap. 8. 17.

**V. 29. ***{He shall not be rich, neither shall his substance continue, neither shall he prolong the perfection thereof upon the earth.}* As his habitation is now become poor, and vile, so his condition shall abide. He may toil and labor till his heart ache, but he shall never attain to his former prosperity again.

*{He shall not be rich}* Some understand it thus; Though he build him famous houses, yet he shall soon be put out of them, and lose all his wealth. He shall not continue always rich, though now he be so. But it is rather to be understood of not getting riches any more, and God’s blasting all his labors to that end; for he is set out as a poor man already, ver. 23.

*{neither shall his substance}* The word חיל, signifies 1. Strength of body, as 2 Sam. 11. 16. 2. Virtue, which is the strength of the mind, Prov. 31. 10. 3. An army or multitude of men, wherein the strength of a nation consists; an host, 1 Sam. 14. 48. 4. Riches, which are the rich man’s fort and strength, Isa. 10. 14. 5. For the fruit of trees, which comes from the strength, and is the riches of the trees, Joel 2. 22. 6. For a palace, wherein consists the strength, and lies the wealth of princes, Psal. 122. 7. 7. For a wall, which is the strength, and defense of a city above unwalled villages, Nah. 3. 8. Here it is taken in the fourth sense, for riches.

*{continue}* For this word, see on Chap. 14. 12. Or, *Keep*. If he do get any riches again after his poverty by his painful labor, he shall not keep them long, God will soon make him poor again.

*{neither shall he prolong}* Heb. *stretch out*. A metaphor from the hand or arm stretched out at length.

*{the perfection thereof}* He shall not come to any great degree of riches. God will blast him before he get a large estate.

*{upon the earth}* See on Chap. 9. 24.

**V. 30. ***{He shall not depart out of darkness, the flame shall dry up his branches, and by the breath of his mouth shall he go away.}* The trouble that now is upon him, he shall never get out of; but shall endure a perpetual night of affliction, till God take him away by a violent death.

*{He shall not depart}* He would willingly remove out of this sad condition, but can find no way of escaping.

*{out of darkness}* Darkness of trouble. See on ver. 22. Or darkness of death; see on ver. 23.

*{the flame}* The flame of troubles, or diseases, or pains, or enemies, all which burn like fire, Isa. 43. 2. Jer. 48. 45. Lam. 2. 3.

*{shall dry up}* Shall quite extinguish or destroy, as the fire does the wood.

*{his branches}* His children, yea his little sucking ones, as the word may import. Or all his wealth, if he recover any again. A metaphor from a tree thunderstruck, whose green branches are dried up and blasted. Or from enemies, who burn all where they come, trees and all, Joel 1. 19. Zech. 11. 1. Esay 29. 6.

*{and by the breath}* See on Chap. 15. 2.

*{of his mouth}* See on verse 5. of this chapter. And, Chap. 4. 9.

*{shall he go away}* Remove out of this world. He shall be slain by some judgment pronounced against him by God in his anger, and sent upon him to make an end of him, as easily and as suddenly, as breath comes out of a man’s mouth. Some understand it of God’s mouth, who was mentioned, verse 25. Others of the wicked man’s own mouth. He should by his words bring himself into capital danger, and so lose his life. Or, He should be so choleric, and impatient in his trouble, that he should send out his last breath suddenly in a passionate fit. He shall die suddenly without sickness or warning, Psal. 146. 4. Isa. 2. 22.

**V. 31. ***{Let not him, that is deceived, trust in vanity: for vanity shall be his recompence}* Eliphaz here begins the conclusion, of his eloquent speech with a proverb, or famous sentence, obliquely accusing Job, of trusting too much to his former prosperity, and persuading him to do so no more, if ever God should restore him to prosperity again, seeing he had been deceived by it already. The misery of such deluded persons, he sets down plainly, verse 32. and by divers elegant metaphors in the verses following, to the end of the chapter.

*{Let not him that is deceived}* The word תעה, literally signifies to err, or go out of the right way; metaphorically to sin, or, go out of God’s way, as Psal. 119. 176. And because, men are often misled by others, therefore it signifies also, to be deceived. It may be taken in general, here, for any wicked man; for they all err, and are deceived: Or, it may be taken particularly for a man misled by riches, and his own corruption; And then it is likely, he thought Job to be such an one, that had abused his prosperity, and been misled by it.

*{trust}* See on Chap. 4. 18.

*{in vanity}* In riches, which are but vain, and cannot uphold him. They are but empty things, and fail in the time of need, Prov. 11. 4. Psal. 119. 37.

*{for vanity}* For he shall by experience find the vanity of them, being made poor.

*{shall be his recompence}* Heb. *his exchange*. He shall change one vanity for another: The vanity of riches for poverty, which he shall see to be vain, and empty, when his hopes are all disappointed, and he left helpless.

**V. 32. ***{It shall be accomplished before his time, and his branch shall not be green}* He goes on by similitudes, to set out the sad, and sudden destruction of ungodly men. 1. From the similitude of a tree, in general in this verse. 2. Of a vine, and olive tree in particular, ver. 33. 3. Of a fire consuming things suddenly, and irrecoverably, ver. 34. 4. Of a woman with child, or rather conceiving herself to be so, and being deceived, verse 35.

*{It}* The drying up of his branch, mentioned ver. 30. and repeated in this verse; or vanity, or his recompense, or change, mentioned ver. 31.

*{shall be accomplished}* The time shall quickly come, when he shall be destroyed. See on Chap. 15. 2. Or, *It shall be cut off*.

*{before his time}* Heb. *in not his day*. He shall be taken away before he need to die by the course of nature. His wickedness shall shorten his life, Eccl. 7. 17. Chap. 22. 16. Psal. 55. 23. or his prosperity shall be gone before his life. His wealth shall not last till his death, as it often does with other men. *His day*, is taken sometimes for the day of his birth, as Chap. 3. 1. Sometimes for the day of man’s death, 1 Sam. 26. 10. Psal. 37. 13. Chap. 18. 20. So here.

*{and his branch}* It comes from a word, that signifies to be bowed or crooked, for boughs either grow so naturally, or are bowed down by the weight of the fruit that grows on them. The word before, ver. 30. signified the lesser boughs, like sucking children: this may signify the greater boughs laden and bowed down with fruit. As sometimes wicked men’s prosperity is interrupted, when they begin to thrive, so at other times when they are very rich.

*{shall not be green}* That is, shall be dried up. His life, or children, or riches, shall be suddenly taken away by some exemplary judgment; as the boughs of a tree blasted before it be old.

**V. 33. ***{He shall shake off his unripe grape, as the vine, and shall cast off his flower, as the Olive.}* It seems that vines and olive trees were frequent in those countries, and had their buds and blossoms sooner blown off than other trees in regard of their tenderness. For such it was fittest for Job to instance in. The scope is, That a wicked man’s wealth and possessions shall be taken away before they come to their height, as fruit before it be ripe. And it is not improbable, that Eliphaz strikes at the sudden and violent taking away of Job’s children, and cattle, Chap. 1.

*{He}* God, as ver. 30. See there. Or, The wicked man himself, as the trees drop their blossoms sometimes, when there is no storm, nor no man to pull them off. And then it has reference to him that is deceived, ver. 31. Or, Impersonally, *It shall be shaken off*. So the third person is often used. See on Chap. 3. 3.

*{shake off}* Pull it off, or beat it off by violence. *Broughton* reads it, *snap off*. The meaning is, that as a violent storm snaps off unripe figs, and blossoms of olives, so shall God by some extraordinary judgment bring ungodly men to poverty. The word signifies to take away by violence, as thieves and robbers do, and as Job’s cattle were taken away before.

*{his unripe grape}* The wicked man’s wealth, which like an unripe grape is not yet come to perfection.

*{as the vine}* Rather, *as of a vine*. For the vine does not shake off her grapes, but the wind or tempest does it. So God destroys wicked men’s wealth. So *Broughton* reads it, *as the vines*, That is, God will as easily strip such of their riches, as he does vines of their unripe grapes.

*{and shall cast off}* Or, *cast away*, as Isa. 2. 20. For it is rather spoken of God, than of the olive tree itself.

*{his flower}* His wealth; as before. So Isa. 18. 5.

*{as the olive}* Or, *as the olives*. See on, *as the vine*, before in this verse.

**V. 34. ***{For the Congregation of hypocrites shall be desolate, and fire shall consume the tabernacles of bribery.}* Here is the second similitude, which sets out the sudden, violent and utter destruction of wicked men. As a fire seizing on an house or city, spares nothing, but devours men, women, children, goods, houses, and leaves places desolate, that were inhabited before; so will God deal with ungodly men. He will bring them to extreme poverty, and leave them nothing at all. Thus had God dealt with Job, and therefore Eliphaz conceives him to be but an hypocrite.

*{For}* They deserve to be ruined, because they are but hypocrites at the best, and very corrupt persons taking bribes.

*{the congregation}* Not one hypocrite alone, but the whole knot of them, that use to meet together, as merchants on the exchange, to plot, how they may oppress the poor, and ruin others. Or, Not himself only, but all his train also and family, children, and servants. Or, his riches and great estate which he has gathered together.

*{of the hypocrites}* Heb. *of the hypocrite*. That is, of every hypocrite. So verse 20. the wicked man. And so he speaks of him, all along, in the singular number, intending, that no wicked man can escape God’s judgment, or looking upon Job in particular, with an evil eye, as on an hypocrite. For the word, see on Chap. 8. 13.

*{shall be desolate}* His companions, family, riches, though never so many, shall become like a desolate wilderness. He shall be left alone. So some read it; *The congregation of the hypocrite shall be as one desolate.* For the adjective is masculine, and agrees not with *congregation* in gender, in the original. See on Chap. 3. 7. See also Chap. 30. 3. Isai. 49. 21.

*{and fire}* אש is taken from a word, that signifies to be strong, for fire has a great force, and will devour the strongest places. It is used metaphorically oftentimes in Scripture, to express divers things, as first, trouble, affliction, sickness, pains, which devour men’s strength, or wealth, Psal. 66. 12. Secondly, War, or enemies, making spoil in a country, as fire does, where it prevails, Jer. 48. 45. Thirdly, for a hot burning blasting wind, which dries up pastures, Joel 1. 20. Here it is taken in the first sense. So *flame* is used, ver. 30.

*{shall consume}* Heb. *shall eat*. It shall feed on their tabernacles as greedily, as a hungry man does on his meat to nourish him, and devour them quite. So Amos 1. 4. Nah. 3. 15. Joel 1. 19. Zech. 11. 1. Deut. 5. 25. 2 King. 1. 12. Chap. 20. 26. So the sword eats up and devours, 2 Sam. 2. 26. The land devours the inhabitants, Num. 13. 32.

*{the tabernacles}* See Chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

*{of bribery}* Of men that take bribes. The houses where such men dwell. The habitations of corrupt judges, or magistrates. So, tents or houses of wickedness, Psal. 84. 10. that is, where wicked men dwell, as appears by the opposition to God’s house there. Or, houses built by bribes received, to pervert justice. Heb. *tabernacles of a bribe*. He casts it in Job’s teeth, that his estate was gotten by indirect means, and his house built by bribes: and conceives, that therefore those judgments came on him, Chap. 1.

**V. 35. ***{They conceive mischief, and bring forth vanity, and their belly prepareth deceit}* The third similitude, to set out the destruction of ungodly men, is in this verse, taken from a woman with child. The mind of the wicked man is compared to the belly of the woman: his hurtful thoughts are compared to conception, and the trouble he puts himself to, that he may bring his cruel plots to effect, is compared to childbearing. Or, it may be, from a woman that thinks she has conceived, and is deceived, having either a tympany, or some false conception in her womb. She pleases herself with the thoughts of a child, but brings forth nothing but water, or some dead mass. So does the hypocrite think he shall undo others, and enrich himself by his crafty plots, but they come to nothing.

*{They conceive}* They imagine, or devise in their hearts.

*{mischief}* They study how to breed labor and sorrow to others. For this monstrous conception, see Psal. 7. 14. Isai. 59. 4.

*{and bring forth}* His outward actions are like his conceptions, hurtful to others, or at least, not profitable to himself.

*{vanity}* Their plots come to nothing in the end. Or, *iniquity*: They undo others, and therefore must be undone themselves, ver. 34.

*{and}* Or, *though*. As it is translated, Psal. 78. 23. For the preparing doubtless went before the bringing forth, and answers to the tarrying of the children in the womb till the time of delivery. Or, *For their belly hath prepared deceit*. For what is in the belly, must come forth by birth. So ו is translated *For*, Chap. 11. 4.

*{their belly prepareth}* For the several significations of the word, see on ver. 2. of this chapter. But here it is taken figuratively for the inward part of man, or mind, which is in the middle of him, as the belly is in the midst of the body. For the mind nourishes the thoughts, as the belly the child, till it be born, Prov. 22. 18. *within thee*. Heb. *in thy belly*, that is, in your mind. And it imports the perpetual thinking of their plot, till it comes to action, as conceiving sets out the first invention of it. This is called preparing in the belly, that is, fitting it for execution.

*{deceit}* He plots how to deceive others, whom he cannot overcome by might.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1. ***{Then Job answered, and said}* Eliphaz had been bitter to Job in this second conflict, more than in the former, and given no comfort at all, as in the former; for there he persuaded him to return to God, promising much happiness to him, if he did so; but here he speaks of nothing, but of the destruction of wicked men; as if he thought Job irrecoverable. Job in his answer, in this chapter and the next, sets out his great grief copiously; defends his innocency, accuses his friends of inhumanity. He charges them with unkindness, in repeating the same things again, and again, which he had answered before, and they still reiterated to no purpose, but to grieve him, as he conceives. First, he shows the vanity, and uncomfortableness of their consolations, to ver. 6. of this chapter; and then falls upon the matter, to refute their false imputations fathered on him, by reason of his sad afflictions; Job therefore contends more eagerly for his integrity, than formerly, and asserts it more plainly, and fully. He first rejects their sayings, and consolations, as unprofitable, troublesome, and reproachful, in the three first verses of this chapter. Then by the contrary he shows, verse 4. and 5. that he would take a far other course with them, if they were in his case, and would be a far better comforter.

*{Then}* See on Chap. 4. 1.

*{answered}* The word ענה signifies. 1. To speak, or, begin a speech, Job 3. 2. 2. To cry out, or speak aloud, Isai. 13. 22. 3. To sing; Psal. 147. 7. 4. To speak in witness bearing, Exod. 20. 16. 5. To hear what is said, or regard it, Job 19. 7. 6. To give an answer to what is said, Job 8. 1. 7. To grant what is desired, which is a real answer, Psal. 18. 41. 8. To be afflicted, Psal. 116. 10. Here it is taken in the sixth sense: for Job answers Eliphaz his former speech.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 3. 2. The word אמר is used, 1. For uttering the thoughts of a man’s mind by speech, Chap. 3. 2. 2. For commanding or speaking imperiously, Est. 1. 10. 3. For speaking proverbially, Gen. 22. 14. 4. For thinking or speaking in heart, Psal. 39. 1. & 53. 1. Chap. 1. 5. See there. Here it is used in the first sense, for Job’s expressing in words what he thought of Eliphaz his painted speech.

**V. 2. ***{I have heard many such things: miserable comforters are ye all}* As if Job should have said, Is it any new thing, which you speak, or have I not heard these things very often? Is this which you do even all the sort of you, is this I say, to comfort your friend, or is it not rather to vex and grieve him to the very heart?

*{I have heard}* For this word, see on Chap. 13. 17.

*{many such things}* Heb. *many things like these*. See Chap. 12. 3. & 13. 1, 2. I have heard many such things as you tell me, of the destruction of ungodly men, from others, and from you, who often inculcate the same things to trouble me. These things are common, and known to all, which you think to be such hidden mysteries. Or I have had many such reproaches cast upon me by others, and often repeated by you in your vain discourses. And therefore he calls them *miserable comforters*, and wishes for an end of their speeches. Here he sets out the needlessness of their many words. Others read it, *I have heard such things often*, or, *many times*. And the words will bear it.

*{miserable comforters}* Heb. *comforters of misery, or trouble*, Chap. 13. 4. It may be an answer to what Eliphaz accused Job of, Chap. 15. 11. namely of setting light divine consolations, which God gave by the mouths of his friends. Nay, says Job, you are well conceited of your counsels, that they are comfortable, but I find no such sweetness in them, but rather more trouble from them. You come to comfort me, but instead of lightening my burden, you lay more load on me. You press me down with urging me, that my miseries arise from grievous sins committed by me. Thus he shows their speeches to be troublesome, and retorts the same thing on Eliphaz and his other friends, which Eliphaz had closely laid on Job’s shoulders, Chap. 15. 35. where the same word עמל is used, and shows, that they were the devisers of mischief, and not he: and that those things which they prate of the violent destruction of ungodly men, as they were not always true, so they were not fit salves for his sore, who being a godly man, had more need to be comforted by God’s promises in his trials.

*{are ye all}* No one of you better than another. You all conspire together to grieve and vex me.

**V. 3. ***{Shall vain words have an end? or what emboldeneth thee, that thou answerest?}* Bildad had objected this persisting in vain and empty words to Job, Chap. 8. 2. And Eliphaz in the beginning of his second conflict with him, Chap. 15. 2, 3. Now he retorts it upon Eliphaz again, who last objected it to him.

*{Shall, &c.}* Heb. *Shall there be an end to words of wind?* You should not speak thus to me, or at least quickly give over speaking so, when I have showed your folly, and not reply upon me again. As if he had said, When will you Eliphaz make an end of such childish speeches? Or, I would to God you would at length make an end of such senseless talk, for I am weary of hearing it.

*{vain words}* Empty of solid reason, like the wind, that has no solidity in it. Or proud arrogant words, that rage like the wind at sea in a tempest, or make you swell like a bladder blown up. He may allude to Eliphaz his high and contumelious speeches, as well as empty. See for these words, and this phrase, Chap. 6. 26. & 8. 2. & 15. 2, 3.

*{have an end}* It comes from a word which signifies to cut off. He would have him make an end of babbling to no purpose.

*{or what emboldeneth thee}* What substantial, and sure grounds have you, or solid reason to confirm what you say? Sure none at all.

*{that thou answerest?}* That you do persist in answering, or answer again, having been confuted before; Or, that you answer thus boldly, and confidently without any ground of reason. For the word *Answering*, see on ver. 1.

**V. 4. ***{I also could speak as ye do, if your soul were in my soules stead, I could heap up words against you, and shake my head at you}* In this verse and the next, Job shows how different his carriage should be from theirs, if they were in misery, and he were to comfort them. He would deal more lovingly with them, but not out of want of wit, for he could speak as curiously and furiously as they, as he shows in this verse; but for want of will to discourage them, and willingness to encourage them, as appears in the next verse.

*{also}* This particle גם, sometimes imports a distribution, as 1 Sam. 28. 15. 2. A conjunction of things, Jer. 14. 18. 3. A comparison of things in a way of likeness or equality, Jer. 51. 49. 4. An augmentation or increase of a thing, Exod. 4. 10. Here it is used in the third sense, as if he had said, I could scoff as eloquently as you do.

*{I}* The pronoun is added in the original for emphasis’ sake, which needed not else, because the person is included in the verb I, whom you now trample under foot, could do as much to you in your misery.

*{could speak}* This shows, that his forbearing to reproach them in their miseries, proceeded not out of want of wisdom to speak elegantly, but out of a good conscience, and commiseration of other men’s miseries, ver. 5. which yet they showed not to him in his troubles.

*{if}* The word לו, sometimes signifies, Would to God, or Oh that, and imports wishing, as Isa. 64. 1. So some would read it *here*. I would you were miserable as I am, and I in prosperity as you are, you should then see, how I could play upon you, as you do on me. But this agrees not with Job’s scope; who was not so malicious as to wish them in that condition, and professes in the next verse, that he would comfort them if they were. 2.
