It signifies, Peradventure, or, it may be, as Gen. 50. 15. 3. It signifies, I pray you, as Gen. 23. 13. 4. It signifies, If, as Ezek. 14. 15. Judg. 13. 23. And so here.

*{your soul were in my souls stead}* For the various significations of the word, see on Chap. 14. 22. Here it signifies the person, If you were afflicted as I am, or in as much danger, as my life is, 1 King. 20. 39, 42. 2 King. 10. 24. Deut. 19. 21.

*{I could heap up words against you}* Heb. *I could joyn against you in words*. I could speak as much against you, as you have done against me. It is an easy thing to find plenty of words to grieve them that are miserable.

*{and shake my head at you}* Heb. *and shake against you with my head*. In despite and mockery, whereof that gesture is a sign. I could deride you in words, and despise you in gestures, as you do me, 2 King. 19. 21. Psal. 22. 7. & 44. 14. Lam. 2. 15. Jer. 18. 16. Others read it *interrogatively, Would I speak as you do, &c*.? No, I would comfort you, ver. 5. It is evident that Job would not have done so as they did: but only told them what he could do, to show them their error. He might upon as good grounds contemn and deride them, if they were in his case, as they do him now. Yet they would think it hard dealing, and so might he now. A good way to bring them to see their sin in another, which, blinded with self-love, they could not see in themselves.

**V. 5. ***{But I would strengthen you with my mouth, and the moving of my lips should asswage your grief.}* Though I could mock at your sorrows, yet I would not do it. But if time and occasion were offered, I would perform the office of a comforter to you, much better than you do to me. I would labor to strengthen you with comfortable words, and with gentle speeches, to ease and assuage your grief.

*{But I would strengthen you}* In the former verse he told them what he could do: here he tells them what he would do. I would be so far from discouraging you with my words, that I would labor to comfort you, and raise up your drooping spirits. A thing which men in miseries have much need of, Isa. 35. 4, 5. Psal. 27. 14. Chap. 4. 4.

*{with my mouth}* See on Chap. 15. 5.

*{and the moving of my lips}* A periphrasis of speaking, for men cannot speak without the motion of the lips. For the word *Lips*, see on Chap. 15. 6.

*{should asswage your grief}* Heb. *should forbid*, or, *restrain*. A metaphor, as some think, from those that assuage men’s pains by incantation. So would he allay their griefs by kind words. Others understand it of holding in unkind and uncomfortable words; for the words, *your grief*, are not in the original. I would not give you so many snaps and girds, as you have given me.

**V. 6. ***{Though I speak, my grief is not asswaged; and though I forbear, what am I eased?}* Job now acknowledges, that all those things had befallen him, which Eliphaz had said, did use to befall ungodly men, and instances in divers particular afflictions, from this verse, to verse 17. yet denies himself to be an ungodly man, and proves his assertion by divers testimonies, from verse 17. to the end of the chapter. He begins this description of his troubles, with the continual vexation his pain brought upon him, which molested him both when he spoke, and when he held his peace. As if he had said, If I speak, my complaint does no good, and if I sit still, and hear your discourses, I receive no comfort, but more vexation. I know not which way to turn me for ease. For if I speak, my torment will not thereby at all be lessened; and if I hold my peace, how much shall my misery thereby be diminished? Psal. 32. 3, 4. Or, If I speak to maintain mine innocency, you say my afflictions prove the contrary. If I hold my tongue, you think by my silence, I acknowledge my guiltiness, as having nothing to say for myself.

*{Though}* Heb. *If*. But it may fitly be translated, *though*, as it is Chap. 9. 15.

*{I speak}* If I plead my cause, and defend my innocency. So the word is used, Chap. 13. 3. Psal. 5. 4. Or, If I complain of my pain.

*{my grief}* By reason of my sores, or of your sore words. Or, *my pain*. For the word signifies both grief of mind, and pain of body.

*{is not asswaged}* See on verse 5. It does not cease, nay, it is not at all lessened.

*{and though I forbear}* To speak, argue, or complain. Whether I give my sorrow a vent, or keep it in, all is one, I find no ease.

*{what am I eased}* Heb. *what goeth from me?* What part of my grief goes away by speaking, or silence? Not a dram, nay, not a scruple. My sorrow will not be a whit the less. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 20.

**V. 7. ***{But now he hath made me weary: thou hast made desolate all my company}* In setting out his misery here, he alludes to Eliphaz his words, Chap. 15. 34. as if he thought he struck at him there in particular, in those words, which he speaks of hypocrites in general. And it is as if he had said, Whatsoever my estate be, and whatsoever I am able to allege for myself, God has wearied me with miseries, and overwhelmed me with calamities: you, I say, O God, have wasted and destroyed, both myself, and all that I had. So that he now begins to set out his troubles, and amplifies them by figures and hyperboles, to move God, and his friends to pity him, and to show, that he, suffering such great miseries, complains not without cause. And laboring between two extremes, whether he should express, or suppress his sorrows, he inclines to the former, and now seeks some ease by complaining.

*{But}* I am so far from being eased by speech, or silence, that my pain increases.

*{now}* God has dealt more kindly with me formerly, but now he lays load on me.

*{he}* God: Spoken to in the next words, and named, as the chief cause of his troubles, in the continuance of Job’s complaint, ver. 11. So the antecedent is named after the relative, Psal. 87. 1, 2. And it argues a great indignation, as if all men knew the author of his troubles, though he did not name him. So passionate may God’s saints be sometimes in their grief. So God is understood. Or, the enemy, ver. 9. Or, *it*; that is, my grief, verse 6. Or, your harsh carriage, ver. 2. &c. But it is most likely he means it of God, and so sets out the author of his sorrows here, and the effects of it after.

*{hath made me weary}* With those manifold afflictions, pressing me so sore, ver. 6.

*{thou}* Now he turns his speech directly to God, and charges him with his miseries: before he spoke but obliquely of him to his friends: *He hath made me weary.* Now he says directly, *Thou hast made desolate all my company.* This shows, that he meant God in the former part of the verse, though he named him not. And it may be, he might look up, or point to heaven, when he said, He has wearied me, as Chap. 19. 26. where it is likely he pointed to his body, when he said. *Worms destroy this.* So Chap. 13. 28. 1 Cor. 15. 53, 54. Thus men in grief, sometimes complain to others of them, whom they conceive to wrong them, and sometimes tell them of it to their faces.

*{hast made desolate}* Laid waste. Deprived me of all my people. See on Chap. 15. 34.

*{all my company}* My family, children, attendants, as Chap. 15. 34. You have left me none of mine to comfort me in my misery. A great addition to trouble. His wife was left, but for a vexation. Satan, by his commission, might have taken her away, as well as his children, Chap. 1. 12. and doubtless he would have done so, had he not kept her alive to vex Job.

**V. 8. ***{And thou hast filled me with wrinkles, which is a witness against me: and my leanness rising up in me, beareth witness to my face}* He now sets out the effects of his pain and grief, the wrinkles and leanness, which appeared in his face, showing that it is not with him, as Eliphaz said it was with ungodly men, Chap. 15. 27. He had no fat, nor collops of fat on him. Job confesses that he neither could, nor would deny, that God’s hand was heavy upon him: for if he should, the wrinkles in his face, would sufficiently witness it, and the leanness which disfigured all his body, would manifestly show it.

*{And thou hast filled me with wrinkles}* You have so consumed my flesh with sores and pains, that nothing is left but my skin, and that is contracted into wrinkles: the usual fruit of great and long sickness. It is translated, cutting off, Chap. 22. 16. But that suits not well with this place, for Job was yet alive.

*{which is a witness against me}* That my griefs are not feigned or counterfeit, nor my troubles small, but very great, verse 6. Chap. 10. 17. for they have worn me away. Or, that you are very angry with me, and do punish me as an ungodly man. By laying these sad strokes upon me, you do confirm the evil opinion my friends have of me, who have urged these my heavy pressures, as a testimony of your indignation against me.

*{and my leanness}* The falling away of my flesh, by continuance of my sores and sorrows.

*{rising up}* Taking rise from my pains within. Or, like a witness coming from his seat, and standing up to give in evidence against me, Psal. 35. 11. For he continues still in his allusion to a judiciary way. Or, *being in me*. So arising, is taken for being, Matth. 11. 11. Or, *standing in me*, that is, abiding with me so long. For the use of the Hebrew word, see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{in me}* In my body. Or, *against me*: To wit, to bear witness against me, as follows in this verse. So ב is used, Num. 23. 23.

*{beareth witness}* Deposes, as a witness before a judge. For the original word, see on verse 1. and Chap. 15. 6.

*{to my face}* Openly against me. It goes not behind my back to tell me so, Chap. 1. 11. My lean body, being like an anatomy, does as a witness produced against a man, and brought face to face to convince him, testify that which my friends accuse me of, that you count me a wicked man, because you set me below the condition of ordinary men. Or, *In my face*. which being most conspicuous to all men, shows forth my leanness most of all. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word *countenance*.

**V. 9. ***{He teareth me in his wrath, who hateth me: he gnasheth upon me with his teeth; mine enemy sharpeneth his eyes upon me.}* He sets out God, as one angry with him, by divers effects of wrath, after the manner of men. An angry man tears his enemy with his hands, gnashes at him with his teeth, looks angrily upon him, makes all his parts show all his displeasure. So much hurt had God done to him, as such a wrathful man would do to his enemy. He does all this out of the sense of the flesh, prevailing against the spirit, and out of the greatness of his grief. But when the spirit prevails, Job is in another temper. Then God shall be his salvation, Chap. 13. 16. and his witness against his scorning friends, verse 19, 20. of this chapter. But now Job cannot satisfy himself, in expressing the greatness of his grief, and of the plagues which God had laid upon him. So sensible are God’s children of his heavy displeasure, when he afflicts them in an extraordinary manner. It lies so sad upon their spirits, that no words will serve to vent their sorrow.

*{He}* Some understand it of Satan, who had a commission to afflict Job. But it may be, that was more than Job knew. Others, of Eliphaz, who tore him with bitter words, as having spoken oftener against him, than the rest, and most bitterly last of all, Chap. 15. Psal. 35. 15, 16. Others, of some principal adversary among those, mentioned, ver. 20. and styled the enemy here, as they conceive. Others, think Job speaks like a man distracted, or one wounded in the dark, who cries out against his enemy out of sense of pain, but knows not who it is. But sure it is meant of God, whom Job often complains of, for laying these afflictions on him. As ver. 7, 8. and elsewhere.

*{teareth me} Me*, is not in the original, and it may well be omitted. God is compared to a lion, that tears his prey to pieces, as soon as he lays hold on it, Hos. 5. 14. Psal. 50. 22. Lam. 3. 10, 11. Psal. 7. 3. Chap. 10. 16.

*{in his wrath}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5, 13. & 14. 13. Others read it, *His wrath teareth me*: because there is no *in*, in the original.

*{who hateth me}* Heb. *and he hateth me*. His blows upon me are so heavy, that they may seem to come from inward hatred, Gen. 49. 23. & 50. 15. Chap. 33. 10. of this book.

*{he gnasheth upon me with his teeth}* Or, *against me*. In derision, or contempt, or as a sign that he will destroy me, Psal. 35. 16. Like a wild beast, ready to devour a poor lamb, and preparing the instruments of his destruction, sharpening his teeth, &c. And by gnashing his teeth, threatening destruction, Psal. 37. 12. The Hebrew word שן, 1. Signifies any tooth, Exod. 21. 24. 2. Ivory, or an elephant’s tooth, 1 King. 10. 18. 3. The crag of a rock, which is sharp like a tooth, Chap. 39. 28. Here it is used in the first sense.

*{mine enemy}* God, who carries himself as an enemy to me, and puts me to straits.

*{sharpeneth his eyes upon me}* Makes them to sparkle for anger, like a glittering sword, Psal. 7. 12. Before he do pierce me through with his sword, he does it with his eye, and terrifies me, as a sword new whetted, and drawn forth by an enemy.

**V. 10. ***{They have gaped upon me with their mouth, they have smitten me upon the cheek reproachfully, they have gathered themselves together against me.}* Job in the seven following verses, reckons up divers fruits of God’s wrath kindled against him. As first of all, that his friends are permitted to blast his reputation, by occasion of his troubles, in this verse. Hereupon he takes occasion to repeat his troubles past, verse 11, 12. and present, verse 13, 14, 15, 16. Job’s complaint against his friends here is very bitter. As if he had said, They have leaped upon me violently with open mouths, as if they would swallow me down at one morsel; and setting upon me, all at once, with mocks and revilings, have buffeted me, as it were, and thumped me about the face.

*{They}* Wicked men, who were God’s instruments to disgrace him. He speaks in the third person, *They*, but he might have said, *Ye*. For it is likely he meant his three friends, who had condemned him for a wicked man. He pays them in their own coin. They, under general terms, condemned him for an hypocrite, and he, under general terms, sets them out for scoffers. And he speaks in the plural number, They, not He: because he aimed not at Eliphaz alone, who spoke last, but at the other two also, who spoke before, and were bitter against him, as well as Eliphaz.

*{have gaped upon me with their mouth}* As a wild beast opens his mouth wide to swallow down his prey, so have they sought to destroy my good name by reproaches and slanders. The phrase sometimes imports a great desire of taking the life away, as Psal. 22. 12, 13. Sometimes, a strong endeavor to blast a man’s reputation, as Psal. 35. 20, 21. Lam. 2. 15, 16. So it is taken here. See ver. 20. This fault he often charges his friends withal, to wit, a desire to ruin his reputation: as Chap. 6. 27. & 12. 4. & 17. 2. & 19. 22. For the word, *mouth*, see on Chap. 15. 5.

*{they have smitten me on the cheek reproachfully}* Smiting on the cheek, was a token of the greatest disgrace, not so much in regard of the greatness of the blow, or wound, as of the dignity of that part of the body, which is smitten, to wit, the face, 2 Cor. 11. 20. Psal. 3. 7. Mic. 5. 1. Lam. 3. 30. 1 King. 22. 24. His meaning is, My friends have disgraced me as much with slanders, as if they had openly struck me upon the face, Jer. 18. 18.

*{they have gathered themselves together against me}* Not only severally mocked me, but come together with a purpose to do it, Psal. 35. 15. It is likely he aims at his three friends, who uphold one another in censuring him for a wicked man. Or, *They have filled themselves upon me*; That is, They have glutted themselves with my sorrows, Exod. 15. 7. Or, They have filled themselves with matter to plead against me. Or, *They come by full troops upon me*.

**V. 11. ***{God hath delivered me to the ungodly, and turned me over into the hands of the wicked.}* Here it is manifest, that he charges God, as the author of his troubles, and it appears hence, that he meant him, ver. 7, 8.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{hath delivered me}* Heb. *hath shut me up*. See on Chap. 11. 10. & 12. 14. Here is one word included in another. He has delivered me into the hands of wicked men, as jailers to shut me up in prison. See on Chap. 15. 8. So Psal. 68. 18. *Thou hast received gifts for men*, that is, to give to men, as it is expounded, Eph. 4. 8. So also Prov. 25. 22. *Thou shalt heap coales of fire on his head.* Heb. *Thou shalt take coales of fire on his head*; that is, You shall take them off from the hearth, and put them upon his head.

*{to the ungodly}* The singular for the plural. To ungodly men, as appears by the end of this verse, and in the verses following. He has given me into the hands of Chaldeans and Sabeans to spoil me, Chap. 1.

*{and turned me over}* Made me to fall into their hands.

*{into the hands}* Given me into their power to strip me of all I had. See on Chap. 1. 12. & 15. 23.

*{of the wicked}* See on Chap. 3. 17.

**V. 12. ***{I was at ease, but he hath broken me asunder: he hath also taken me by the neck, and shaken me to pieces, and set me up for his mark.}* As if that were not enough, that God had given him into the hands of wicked men to spoil him, or they had not malice or power enough to hurt him, he charges God in this verse for his greatest enemy. As if he thought, that men could not have done him so much hurt, if God had not joined with them. And it appears, Chap. 1. 16, 19. That the destruction of his sheep, servants, and children, was by fire and wind from heaven, which no man could send, and therefore Job here attributes them to God.

*{I was at ease}* He now exaggerates his present misery by his former felicity. I was quiet, or in a plentiful estate; for outward plenty brings ease of body, and content of mind, Psal. 30. 6. Jer. 49. 31. Zech. 7. 7. Psal. 122. 6, 7. Chap. 19. 6. My sorrow is so much the more, because I am cast out of a prosperous estate, into a miserable one.

*{but he hath broken me asunder}* Some learned men conceive the word to signify breaking of grapes or olives in the fat, to make wine or oil. The weight of calamity had pressed out the juice of all their prosperity, Mr. *Broughton* expresses the sense well, reading it thus; *I was wealthy, but he hath undone me.* And we call undoing men, breaking them, to wit, in their estates.

*{he hath also taken me by the neck}* Like a wrestler, who takes his enemy by the neck, and then flings him down, and wounds him. Heb. *he hath laid hold on my neck*. The word properly signifies the hinder part of the neck, opposite to the throat, which men lay hold on to pull their enemies down, Chap. 25. 26. Sometimes it is used figuratively, to set out an hard heart; as Isai. 48. 4. Sometimes, turning the neck to the enemy, sets out flight in war, Psal. 18. 40. which is translated turning the back, Exod. 23. 27. Here it is taken literally, as was said before.

*{and shaken me to pieces}* He sets out his great pain, by very great and high expressions; of breaking asunder, taking by the neck, shaking him to pieces, shooting at him, as at a mark. Heb. *he hath scattered me*. It signifies to break a thing into the least pieces, and then throw them all abroad: like an earthen pot broken, whereof one piece flies one way, another, another way, so that it cannot be pieced together again. This argues an irrecoverable loss of health and wealth. For so Job thought, his miseries were so great. See Psal. 2. 9. Jer. 23. 29. *And set me up*. Heb. *And made me rise*. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{for his mark}* To shoot all the arrows of his judgments into me. Psal. 38. 2. Job 6. 4. God shot at him as at a mark, when he took away his children and goods, Chap. 1. Chap. 7. 20. Lam. 3. 12. Heb. *for a mark to him*. The Heb. word is taken from a word, that signifies to observe, because all men observe the mark carefully, that they may direct their arrows aright to it. It signifies sometimes a prison, where men are carefully observed, and kept, that they may not escape, as Jer. 38. 28. But that signification will not fit here.

**V. 13. ***{His archers compass me round about, he cleaveth my reins asunder, and doth not spare; he poureth out my gall upon the ground}* He goes on in the similitude of shooting at a mark, or rather of war. God sets wicked men, or rather sores to afflict me who will not miss the mark, but hit home, and shoot the arrows of affliction and pain into me up to the head. He shows the effects of these sharp weapons, and by choice allegories makes it appear that he endures most grievous pains in his inward and most noble parts.

*{His}* God’s instruments appointed by him to afflict me.

*{archers}* The word comes from רבב which signifies both many and great. For in war there are many archers, and in the quiver many arrows to shoot. And Job’s sores as they were many and great, so they had made many and great wounds in him. So this word is used for archers, Jer. 50. 29. It appears that hereby are not meant his friends that mocked him, but his sores (that like sharp arrows had made deep wounds in him) by that which follows in this verse, and those that ensue.

*{compass me round about}* As soldiers go round about an enemy to shoot their arrows into him on every side, that no part of him might escape unwounded. And indeed Job had many arrows shot at him. He had many afflictions upon him. Heaven shot fire against him to destroy his cattle, the air, wind to destroy his children, his wife and friends reproaches to trouble him, and God sores into him, to put him to pain.

*{he cleaveth my reins asunder}* Every one of these archers does it, or God who set them to work. The pain strikes me to the heart. The arrows do not pierce my skin only or flesh, but my inward parts also. He follows me with most sharp and bitter pains. The word כליות for reins in the original comes from a word that signifies desiring, because the reins are the seat of the appetite or desiring faculty. Sometimes it is taken literally for that fleshly part of the body so called. As Lev. 3. 4. The two kidneys, or reins. Sometimes figuratively for the inward thoughts or desires. As Psal. 7. 9. Sometimes metaphorically for grains of corn, which resemble the fashion of the reins, or kidneys. As Deut. 32. 14. Here it is taken literally. As also, Lam. 3. 13.

*{and doth not spare}* He shows no manner of compassion towards me, but goes on with all manner of extremity against me, and inflicts grievous wounds on me. Or, He adds wounds to wounds, and does not cease, Chap. 27. 22. Lam. 2. 2, 21.

*{He poureth out my gall upon the ground}* He has wounded me mortally as it were, like one shot into the gall, the bag whereof being broken kills the party, Chap. 20. 25. So Job made account his sores would certainly kill him. It has his name in the original from bitterness, for nothing in the body is more bitter than it. For the word ground, or earth, see on Chap. 9. 24.

**V. 14. ***{He breaketh me with breach upon breach, he runneth upon me like a giant}* He expounds here, what he meant by not sparing: Inflicting many wounds without pity.

*{He breaketh me with breach upon breach}* Heb. *upon the face of breach*: with continual breaches one in the neck of another without any intermission, Psal. 19. 2. & 42. 7. Either he means his body broke out with fresh sores daily, or alludes to the messengers of his losses coming fast one after another, Chap. 1. 14. &c. From the time he began to afflict me till now he never left tormenting me, nor gave me any rest.

*{he runneth upon me}* As soon as any occasion is offered, he runs upon me presently, as one greedy to destroy me, Chap. 15. 26. Or, *he runneth against me*. To wit, with a weapon in his hand to run me through.

*{like a giant}* Or, *like a strong man*. He has rushed upon me with all his strength, and has showed the uttermost of his power against me, coming on me suddenly as a strong warrior on a weak enemy to destroy him. It comes from a word that signifies to prevail, for strong men or giants use to prevail in war.

**V. 15. ***{I have sowed sackcloth upon my skin, and defiled my horn in the dust}* Job now has relation to Eliphaz his words, Chap. 15. 25. showing that in all these troubles he had not swelled against God, as he had hinted there, but that forgetting his former dignity and prosperity he had abased himself, and put on apparel suitable to his sorrowful condition.

*{I have sowed}* Some interpret it thus, My affliction is so great, that I have not only put on sackcloth, but kept it on still, as if it were sewed or fastened to my skin, as expecting continued troubles. I had once soft apparel, but now harsh, answerable to my condition. Others thus; The sackcloth which I put on, when my great losses befell me, Chap. 1. now cleaves to my skin by reason of my sores, as if it were sewed to it.

*{sackcloth}* Some take it as an act of humiliation, for it was an usual thing even for those that were wealthy to put on sackcloth in token of sorrow, when God’s judgments were upon them, as 1 King. 21. 27. 2 King. 6. 30. Jon. 3. 5. Others think he did it out of extreme poverty, as if he had no better clothes left to wear, and therefore was forced to wear coarse sackcloth, such as men carry corn in, Gen. 42. 25. Josh. 9. 4. It was therefore very troublesome to a man full of sores, as Job now was, Isa. 3. 24. Am. 8. 10.

*{upon my skin}* He wore it next of all to his skin, and therefore it was the more troublesome.

*{and defiled}* Or, *wallowed*. The word signifies an action with contempt.

*{my horn}* My strength, power, and honor, and whatsoever is excellent for me. For the horn is most eminent in many strong beasts, and with it they defend themselves, and assault their enemies. The word קרן signifies, 1. The horn of a beast. Dan. 8. 3. 2. A horn to sound withal, Josh. 6. 5. 3. A vessel of horns to carry oil in to anoint kings, 1 Sam. 16. 1. 4. An hill, which stands higher than the rest of the ground, as a horn is highest in a beast, Isa. 5. 1. 5. Strength, for the strength of many beasts is in their horns, Psal. 75. 10. 6. Kingly power and authority, whereby the king is higher than others, 1 Sam. 2. 10. 7. The honor or outward ornament of a man representing it, Psal. 75. 4. 8. The corners of the altars made like horns, Exod. 29. 12. 9. Brightness or shining like horns, Hab. 3. 4. Here it is taken in the seventh sense for the loss of his dignity, or polluting his fine apparel, as Chap. 2. 8.

*{in the dust}* Psal. 7. 5. For the word see on Chap. 14. 8. on the word *Ground*.

**V. 16. ***{My face is foul with weeping, and on mine eye-lids is the shadow of death}* Neither did I thus abase myself for a vain show, as hypocrites do (howsoever you O Eliphaz do most unworthily account me such an one) seeing this my face is with tears continually trickling down my cheeks beslubbered as it were with dirt: and my eyelids falling down, and waxing dim do represent the very image of death being near at hand.

*{My face}* See on Chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

*{is foul}* Like a troubled water full of dirt, Isa. 57. 20. Lam. 2. 11.

*{with weeping}* Incredible and implacable griefs cause me to pour out abundance of tears, whereof not mine eyes only, but my whole face is a witness, which is all over fouled with them.

*{and on mine eye-lids}* See on Job 3. 9.

*{is the shadow of death}* See on Chap. 3. 5.

**V. 17. ***{Not for any injustice in mine hands: also my prayer is pure}* He had before sought to move them to pity him for the abundance of his sorrows; but now he adds another argument of pity, taken from his innocency. Or, He had before acknowledged, that he felt those miseries, which they said, did use to fall on ungodly men: and now he labors to prove that he was no hypocrite, although God dealt thus sharply with him. As if in other words he had said, I have not deserved to be thus made more miserable than all other men, who have carried myself uprightly towards God and man. Neither is there any cause, why you should inveigh against my former life. For these things are not befallen me for any injury I have done to any man; and as touching religion, I call upon God with a good conscience.

*{not for}* Heb. *for not*. A transposition of the words. So Isa. 53. 9. in the original, where it should be translated, not for any violence he had done. Mr. *Broughton* reads it here, *For no misdoing of my hands*. Hereby Job intimates, that he was not able to comprehend the cause of this his grievous punishment, for he was not guilty of injustice towards men, nor of hypocrisy before God, Chap. 31. 13, 21, 38.

*{any injustice}* Violent dealing, or other injuries done to men.

*{in mine hands}* In mine actions. My hands have not handled any man violently, nor given him cause to complain of me. Injustice is attributed to the hands, because men smite others with the hand, and with their hands take away their goods.

*{also my prayer is pure}* I have worshipped God aright. Thus he takes away what Eliphaz had charged him with, that he restrains prayers before God. Chap. 15. 4. Mr. *Broughton* reads it, *But my wish is clean, saying*. Meaning his desire expressed in the next verse.

**V. 18. ***{Oh earth cover not thou my blood, and let my cry have no place.}* Because this was the great controversy between him and his friends, about his integrity, he calls the creatures in this verse, and God himself in the next to bear witness of it with him. He wishes heavy things may light upon him, if he be guilty of those things, whereof his friends accuse him.

*{O earth}* Seeing my friends will not hear, but condemn me unjustly, I wish the earth could hear. A vehement speech of one, that in his distress finds no help from men, yet wishes it, where it cannot be had, and speaks to the earth, as if it were alive, and could testify for him. Such exclamations to senseless things show, that men fail in their duty, and therefore nature’s frame being as it were out of order in men, all creatures are after a sort wronged in it, and had need to look about them. See the like, Deut. 32. 1. in the beginning of Moses’ song; and Isa. 1. 2. in the beginning of his prophecy. For the word Earth, see on Chap. 9. 24.

*{cover not thou my blood}* Turning his speech unto the earth, he desires that if he be guilty of shedding any man’s blood, or any injury to men, it would reveal it, and cry out against him, so that his prayers may never be heard. The word דם, blood is used, 1. For the blood of living creatures, Levit. 19. 26. 2. For things like blood in color, Joel 2. 31. 3. For murder, or bloodshedding, because the life is in the blood, and departs when the blood is shed, Isa. 26. 21. Deut. 17. 8. Psal. 5. 6. And so it is taken, not for Job’s own blood, but the blood of other men supposed by his friends to be shed by him. Which if it were true, he wishes it might be made known all the world over, that he might be punished according to his deserts. A manner of speaking taken from murderers who use to cover all the tracks of blood shed by them, lest they should be discovered, Gen. 4. 10, 11. Isa. 26. 21. Ezek. 24. 7.

*{and let my cry have no place}* Let God deny to hear me when I call earnestly upon him for help in my great necessities, Chap. 27. 9. Psal. 66. 18. If I cry for mercy, let not God hear me, but punish me as a just judge, Isa. 1. 15.

**V. 19. ***{Also now behold my witness is in heaven, and my record is on high.}* He now calls God himself to witness, and shows, that having so great testimony in his own conscience of God’s knowledge of his integrity, he need not much regard the testimony of men. As if he had said, I do not affirm myself to be innocent of these great crimes you lay to my charge upon my own word, for that were your Ay, and my No: neither do I look that the earth should speak for me, which I know it cannot, but God himself will bear witness of the truth hereof. See the like, Joh. 5. 31, 32.

*{Also}* See on verse 4. of this chapter. Besides men among whom I live, God also knows my integrity.

*{now}* Even at this present time, when you condemn me, God can clear me.

*{behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{my witness}* God, who knows all my thoughts and secret actions, and can clear me of all those evils, you falsely impute unto me.

*{in heaven}* See on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. God is everywhere present, but most gloriously manifests his presence in the highest heavens. There is his court and throne, Isai. 66. 1. and thither are we taught to direct our prayers to him, Matth. 5. 9.

*{and my record}* The same in sense with the former word, witness; only the one is an Hebrew word, and the other a Syrian. The one used by Jacob, the other by Laban, Gen. 31. 47. It is doubled for more assurance. See on Chap. 14. 10, 11.

*{is on high}* Heb. *is in the heights, or high places*. The same in sense with heaven mentioned before, which is higher than any other place. See Chap. 25. 2. & 31. 2. Psal. 148. 1. Matth. 21. 9. Luk. 2. 14. yet it is worth noting, that the Hebrews have another word, when they speak of high places used to sacrifice in, contrary to God’s word. במות. 1 King. 12. 31. And this word מרומים is used, when God or heaven is mentioned, as if God in very words would distinguish between true worship and false.

**V. 20. ***{My friends scorn me, but mine eye poureth out tears unto God.}*

It appears by Job’s confident appeal unto God, that he was well assured of his integrity; else he dared not have done it.

*{My friends}* Seeing you, who as friends should comfort me, condemn me, I appeal to God who can clear me. The original word comes from a word, that signifies to feed, because friends often invite one another, and feed together, Psal. 41. 9. He meant doubtless these three friends that came to comfort him, and now use all their wit and eloquence to discourage him.

*{scorn me}* Heb. *are my scorners*. My sorrow is the more because they are not strangers, which deride me, but friends; having therefore no hope on earth, I put up my prayers to God to clear me. See the like complaint of David, Psalm 109. 4. & 55. 12. The word לוץ sometimes signifies ambassadors, as 2 Chr. 32. 31. Sometimes interpreters, as Job 33. 23. But here it signifies scorners. As also Psal. 119. 51. Prov. 3. 34. & 19. 28.

*{but}* Or, *therefore*. So it is translated, Job 35. 16.

*{mine eye}* I weep in my prayers. And the eye is mentioned, because that is the instrument of weeping. See on Chap. 15. 15. on the word *Sight*.

*{poureth out tears}* Distills them, or puts them forth abundantly. A metaphor from dropping rain, Psal. 119. 28. Prov. 19. 13. I weep plentifully, as men use to do in prayer to God in their extremity. Mine eye does by tears cry out as it were to God, because of the great injuries offered unto me by my friends. Or, Mine eye pours out tears, and I with much sorrow pray to God. For one word included in another, see on Chap. 15. 8. & 16. 11.

*{unto God}* That he may right me against your false accusations. I pray to him with a heavy heart to that purpose. Raindrops fall downward to the earth, but prayer drops go upward to heaven to the throne of God. By God here he means Jesus Christ, whom he desires in the next verse to put up his prayers unto God the Father. For the word, see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

**V. 21. ***{Oh that one might plead for a man with God as a man pleadeth for his neighbour.}* The words thus read carry the face of a discontented wish of Job desiring to plead with God. But word for word they run thus. *And he will plead for a man with God, and the son of man for his friend.* This is, Christ, who is God and man will plead my cause with his father: he can prevail, because he is God equal to the Father, he will undertake it, because he will be man like to me. This interpretation agrees best with the coherence, and the words following. And accordingly we shall interpret the place.

*{and he}* Christ Jesus the mediator between God and man. See in the end of the former verse.

*{will plead}* I know he will plead for me, although you plead earnestly against me. For the word Pleading, see on Chap. 15. 3.

*{for a man}* See on Chap. 3. 3. & 4. 17. & 10. 5. & 15. 10.

*{with God}* With God his Father. One person which is God the Son, with another person which is God the Father, the same God, though a different person in the Trinity. For as אלהים (though plural) is used personally, Psal. 45. verse 6. for God the Son, and verse 7. for God the Father, so אלוה the singular may be used here for God the Son, v. 20. and for God the Father, verse 21. For the word see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{and the Son of man}* Christ who will be in due time incarnate, and become man to save men. This is Christ’s title very frequent in the New Testament, Mat. 8. 20. & 10. 23. & 11. 19. & 12. 8. And it seems that Job knew this mystery of the incarnation of Christ, Chap. 19. 25, 26, 27. where he speaks of him both as God, and as a visible redeemer. For the word Son, see on Chap. 14. 21. For Man, see on Chap. 14. 1.

*{for his friend}* For everyone that believes in him. These he calls his friends. Joh. 15. 13, 14, 15. For the word Friend, see on verse 20.

**V. 22. ***{When a few years are come, then I shall go the way, whence I shall not return}* He being not guilty of a wicked life, shows, that he appeals to God not feignedly, but confidently, and with a sincere heart, speaking as one that were ready to die, even from his heart, or really as he thinks. Or, Seeing he had but a while to live, he desires he might be cleared before he die, that he might not remain in perpetual infamy, and his memory be odious to posterity. This would be a great comfort to him, if God would please to restore him to ease and credit before his death, which could not be long expected, if God relieved him not.

*{when}* Or, *For*. So it is used chap. 7. 13. I wish that God to whom I appeal would clear me quickly. Else it will be too late, when I shall be dead.

*{a few years}* Heb. *years of number*. Numbered out and determined for me to live here on earth. Or rather a small number of years, as it is translated, which may quickly be numbered. For it is very likely that Job thought his troubles would soon make an end of him. So this phrase is used, Gen. 34. 30. Numb. 9. 20. Deut. 4. 27. And on the contrary, very much corn laid up by those, Gen. 41. 49. is said to be without number. See also on Job 15. 20.

*{are come}* Come to an end well-nigh, I must needs die shortly.

*{then I shall}* Or, *when I shall*. So it is translated, 1 Sam. 13. 1.

*{go}* See on Chap. 14. 20. on the word *Passeth*.

*{the way}* The course. A metaphor from travelers.

*{whence I shall not return}* I shall die, and not come back into this world any more. A periphrasis of death, the way of all the earth, Josh. 23. 14. & 1 King. 2. 2. See chap. 5. 9, 10. For the word שוב see on chap. 14. 13. on the word *Past*.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1. ***{My breath is corrupt, my days are extinct, the graves are ready for me.}* He goes on where he left at the end of the former chapter, and proceeds to show, that he draws every day nearer to his end. As if he had said, seeing my breath is passing out of my body, and I have now as it were finished the course of my life, nothing remains, but that I be laid in the grave.

*{My breath}* See on Chapter 15. 2. upon the word *East wind*.

*{is corrupt}* It smells strong and noisomely arguing much corruption within me, chap. 19. 17. Or, *Is drawn painfully*. I breathe, but with much difficulty.

*{My days}* The time of my life: see on Chap. 15. 32. and on chap. 14. 14.

*{are extinct}* They will soon be extinguished. They are like a candle burning in the socket, and ready to go out. I am ready to die. See the like similitude, Prov. 13. 9. This natural moisture as he thought, was well-nigh consumed, and then death follows.

*{The graves are ready}* I have nothing left but to think of my grave and burial. The nearness of his death is hereby intimated, as it was in other words, chap. 16. 12. See the like, Psalm 88. 3, 4. I can endure these pains no longer: I must needs die shortly. And he says graves in the plural number, because every place did as it were proffer a grave to him, and threaten him with death in regard of his many pains. Or by the graves he means, some one grave or other would swallow him up ere long. As Jephthah was buried in the cities of Gilead. That is, in some one of them. Judg. 12. 7. The Messiah must ride on a colt of the asses, that is, of some one ass. Zech. 9. 9. There is another word used in Scripture for the grave. See the difference, chap. 7. 9.

**V. 2. ***{Are there not mockers with me? and doth not mine eye continue in their provocation?}* He now lays down another cause of the shortening of his days besides his sores and pains, which he had mentioned before, to wit, the scoffs and taunts of his friends, who came to comfort him, and yet derided him continually.

*{Are there not}* Sure there are mockers with me. A negative question ordinarily includes an affirmative answer. And it is more emphatical than a bare affirmation. As if he had said, I challenge any man to deny, that there are mockers with me; so 2 Sam. 11. 3. Is not this Bathsheba? Sure it is. This mocking of Job by his friends was a great addition to his sorrows, as appears by his complaint, chap. 16. 20. Heb. *If there be not mockers with me.* Understand; then let God punish me for speaking falsely, see on chap. 1. 11. Yet it may be translated interrogatively, as it is here: Are there not mockers with me? so it is used, chap. 4. 17. & 36. 29.

*{mockers}* Who mock at my innocency and scoff at me, as at a wicked man, chap. 16. 20. & 21. 3. He means his three friends who disputed with him, as appears by the places cited. And that they did not so much mock at his sores; as at his words, which they thought to be absurd and unreasonable, and therefore he first tells them of their scoffing, and then answers their arguments.

*{with me}* He does not mean that they were in his company, and did mock at others, which yet is a grief to a good man to hear others derided: but that they mocked at him: so Psal. 55. 18. There were many with me, that is, many did fight against me, as appears by coherence. And Psal. 85. 4. *Cause thine anger towards us to cease*. Heb. *with us*, that is, against us.

*{and doth not mine eye}* See on chap. 15. 15. upon the word *Sight*. I am compelled to be a looker on, when I am mocked. It is not done behind my back, but before my face, which is the greater grief.

*{continue}* Heb. *Lodge*, or *Tarry all night*. Men go abroad in the day, but lodge at home in the night. Either it may be understood, that they mocked both day and night and gave him no rest at all, or else that their words were so bitter against him in the day, that he could not sleep all night, but wept continually upon the thoughts thereof, chap. 16. 20. Psal. 6. 6. And so they were as it were in his sight, when he was in bed.

*{in their provocation}* Not actively in provoking them, but passively in being provoked by their continued taunts. Provocation here signifies bitter speeches, and provoking words. Psal. 106. 7. They did so irritate him with their words, that he lay awake all night, and could not sleep for thinking what to answer to them.

**V. 3. ***{Lay down now, put me in a surety with thee. Who is he that will strike hands with me?}* An apostrophe to God, as chap. 16. 7. not a speech to *Eliphaz*. The words are ordinarily interpreted by way of a challenge to God, as if Job had required a surety to undertake for God, that he should have satisfaction from him, if he were cast in judgment. But they may be read thus, *Appoint I pray thee my surety with thee; who is he then, that will strike upon my hand?* That is, appoint Christ, who is with you in heaven, and has undertaken to be my surety, appoint him I say, to plead my cause and to stand up for me, and then no man will dare to contend with me. And so it is suitable to the notes on chap. 16. 21. and to Rom. 8. 33.

*{Lay down}* Or, *Appoint*; as Exod. 1. 11. They did set over them taskmasters. That is, they appointed taskmasters to be over them, so it is used also, Exod. 21. 13. Appoint Christ to plead for me.

*{Now}* Or, *I pray thee*. See on chap. 13. 18.

*{Put me in a surety}* Or, *My surety*. So the same word is translated a surety, Prov. 17. 18. And not putting in a surety. And then it depends upon the former words thus, *Appoint I pray thee my surety with thee*. Christ is called a surety, Heb. 7. 22. where the same word that is here, is used in the Syrian Testament.

*{with thee}* Who is with you in heaven ready to plead for me: see on chap. 16. 21.

*{who is he}* I shall need fear no enemy, if Christ take my cause in hand.

*{that will strike hands with me}* Or, *that will strike upon my hand*. It is ordinarily understood of striking hands as a surety for God, that would pass his word, that Job should have a fair trial with him, and satisfaction if it should appear he was wronged. So is it used to express suretyship, Prov. 6. 1. & 17. 18. & 22. 26. But as men did strike hands then in suretyship for money, so it may be they did so in suretyship for trials, and appearance in judgment. And then the meaning is, Who dare contend with me in judgment, if Christ stand up for me?

**V. 4. ***{For thou hast hid their heart from understanding; therefore shalt thou not exalt them.}* The cause why I do so confidently appeal to you in Jesus Christ is, that I know I thereby shall prevail against the accusation of these my friends, whereby they have accused me as an hypocrite before you, and that you will punish these my friends for accusing me falsely, out of gross ignorance. These things are spoken by way of preoccupation to prevent a suspicion of too much boldness in Job.

*{For thou hast hid their heart from understanding}* You have cast such a cloud before the heart of these my friends, who mock me thus, and pronounce me a wicked man because of my troubles, that they cannot rightly understand things. For if they were not blockish, and wanted common understanding, they would clear me themselves. They would see, that good men are subject to heavy afflictions, as well as bad. Eccles. 9. 1. Here is an inversion of the words, and the meaning is, You have hidden understanding from their heart. See Judg. 1. 8. *They set the city on fire*. Heb. *They cast the city into the fire*; that is, they cast fire into the city. So Psal. 74. 7. *They have cast fire into thy Sanctuary*. In the original it is, *They have cast thy Sanctuary into the fire*, and so it might have been translated here, *Thou hast hid understanding from their heart*. And then the words had been more clear. For the word Heart, see on chap. 15. 12. *Therefore shalt thou not exalt them.* That is, you shall cast them down. Meiosis. See on chap. 12. 2. Either you shall cast them down from that high or full estate they are in now, or you being a righteous judge shall pass sentence for me against them, and so cast them down in this cause. Psal. 1. 5. This suits well with what follows verse 5. and with the event, chap. 42. 7.

**V. 5. ***{He that speaketh flattery to his friends, even the eyes of his children shall fail.}* You have all this while flattered God to condemn me, as if you could with fine words make the world believe, that you stood up for God, as though his justice could not be upheld in afflicting me so grievously unless I were an hypocrite. But God will punish you and your posterity for this your gross dealing. See the like charge upon them, chap. 13. 7. And this interpretation agrees well with the former verse. God will surely pull you down because you deal so corruptly in this business. It suits also well with the words following. It is true, such troubles are upon me, as you speak of; but not to prove me an hypocrite, as you say, but for other good ends and purposes. Others take it thus, you tell me of restoration to prosperity again upon repentance, chap. 5. 17. & 8. 5. & 11. 13. But you do but flatter me. My miseries show, that I am past recovery. Others thus, If I should flatter you I should bring a curse upon my posterity. I must therefore tell you plainly of your bad dealing with me, chap. 32. 21, 22. the first is the best, as being most suitable to the context.

*{he that speaketh}* Heb. *Of him that speaketh*. Or, *as for him that speaketh*.

*{flattery}* The word חלק in the original signifies 1. a part of anything divided for portions to divers persons. Psal. 142. 5. 2. It signifies smoothness of anything, because many things to be divided are first made smooth; Gen. 27. 16. 3. it signifies flattering, when men’s words are smooth; Prov. 7. 21. Psal. 55. 21. 4. because smooth things are slippery, and men may quickly slide if they tread on them, it signifies slippery places; Psal. 73. 18. Here it is taken in the third sense for smooth words and flattering speeches. For flattery see Prov. 2. 16. & 5. 3. & 7. 5. & 29. 5. Psal. 5. 9. Isa. 30. 10.

*{to his friends}* See on chap. 16. 20.

*{Even the eyes}* See on chap. 15. 15. on the word *Sight*.

*{of his children}* See on Chapter 14. 21. upon the word *Sons*.

*{shall fail}* God does so hate flattery, that he will not only punish him for it, but his children also. Or, if the punishment of his flattery light not upon him, while he lives, it will be sure to light on his children, when he is dead and gone. See the like, 1 King. 11. 12. & 21. 29.

**V. 6. ***{He hath made me also a by-word of the people, and aforetime I was as a tabret}* His friends might object and say, We neither flatter God, nor wrong you, If you be not wicked, why does God then punish you so grievously? Job answers, first by confessing that which all men saw, and he could not deny, namely, that God had indeed laid heavy afflictions upon him, verse 6, 7. And then shows that it was for other ends than they dreamed of, and that godly men would make better use of his afflictions than they did. In this verse he amplifies his miseries, showing that they were so great, that not only these friends; but also all the people mocked him, even the basest of them, as he speaks, chap. 30. 1.

*{He hath made me also}* Heb. *He hath set me*, or *placed me*. As the word is used, Gen. 30. 38. He does look higher than the people, that scoffed at him, acknowledging that this contempt befell him by the will and counsel of God. As David did in a like case, 2 Sam. 16. 10. And so Job did before in this chapter, verse 4.

*{a by-word}* Or; *a proverb*. To speak proverbs against a man, or to make him a byword argues him to be in the depth of misery, Numb. 21. 27, 28. Deut. 28. 37. Psal. 44. 13, 14. Job was a man scoffed at by all men; and generally accounted most miserable. So we speaking of a man very poor say, He is as poor as Job, or Lazarus. For the word, see on chap. 27. 1. upon the word *Parable*.

*{of the people}* Not of some few men alone, but of the multitude. It comes from a word in the original, that signifies, To cover. For a multitude of men met together do cover the face of the earth.

*{and aforetime}* Or, *to mens faces*. That is, I was openly mocked and derided.

*{I was}* Or, *I am*. If the former word be translated, *openly*, then it will run best thus, *I am openly a Taber*. So Mr. *Broughton* reads it, and the words will bear it, for it is the future tense in the original, which for want of a present tense in Hebrew is put more often for the present time, than for the time past. And it better suits with Job’s condition who sets out his present miseries, and amplifies them by the scoffs of the multitude.

*{as a Tabret}* They not only spoke proverbs in derision of me, but also made songs of my miseries to sing abroad, and play withal upon the tabret, as men use to do in the calamities of famous men. And other men, that hear it, are pleased and laugh at my misery, as at a musical instrument, and songs to make them merry. It is a great grief to men in affliction, to see others laugh, leap, sing, dance, make music of their sorrows, Lam. 2. 15. Psal. 69. 11, 12. The Hebrew word Tophet commonly is the name of the place; where they made their children pass through the fire to Molech, and because they played on tabrets, and such loud instruments then, that they might not hear the noise of their children, it was so called: see 2 King. 23. 10. Jer. 7. 31. But it cannot be so taken here. The whole verse may be read thus, *He hath made me also a by-word of the people, whereas aforetime, I was as a tabret.* That is, I am now a scorn to them, who delighted in me in my prosperity. So he amplifies his misery by calling to mind his former comfortable condition. This he does largely, chap. 29. showing his former prosperity: and chap. 30. his present calamity.

**V. 7. ***{Mine eye also is dim by reason of sorrow: and all my members are as a shadow}* He now sets out his troubles, and sorrows hyperbolically, as if they had made him a blind anatomy, and rather the shadow of a man, than a true man. Man in his prosperity is but as a shadow, Psal. 39. 5, 6. 1 Chron. 29. 15. much more in his misery.

*{mine eye also}* See on Chapter 15. 15. upon the word, *Sight*.

*{is dim}* The word כהה primarily signifies contracted or shrunk up. And it is applied sometimes to the eye, the nerves whereof being shrunk up by sickness or old age, the sight decays, as chap. 16. 16. Psal. 6. 7. Sometimes to wounds, which when they begin to heal, overspread not as before, in the skin, but are contracted, and grow less and less, as Lev. 13. 6. Sometime to the mind, the spirits being contracted or lessened by grief of heart, As Ezek. 21. 7.

*{by reason of sorrow}* See on chap. 6. 2.

*{and all my members}* The word primarily signifies formations or things formed. It does not send us back to our creation, but to our formation in the womb to know our nature, as Psal. 139. 16.

*{are as a shadow}* All my bodily members are so extenuated, and consumed, that they seem to make up rather a picture or shadow of a man, than a true body: for my flesh and strength is gone, and no member is able to do his office.

**V. 8. ***{Upright men shall be astonied at this, and the innocent shall stir up himself against the hypocrite}* In this verse, and that which follows, Job comforts himself in hope that godly men will make a better use of his adversity, than his friends had done. And that although at first they might be astonished to see a man so upright to suffer such heavy pressures, yet they would not condemn him nor neglect their own duties to God, but hate hypocrisy and grow in piety.

*{upright men}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{shall be astonied}* Others read it, *that the upright may wonder*: Others, *let the upright wonder*. The first way sets out the events of Job’s troubles. The second God’s intention in afflicting him. The third, the duty of good men beholding his afflictions. The first interpretation is the best. The word שמם signifies 1. when it is spoken of a thing, to be desolate, as chap. 16. 7. Lam. 5. 18. 2. because such desolations of houses or countries make men wonder, it signifies to be astonished, whereby the mind is as it were laid waste and desolate also, as not being able to give an account of the causes of such desolations, as Isa. 52. 14. So here, they shall wonder at God’s sharp dealing with me, but yet not condemn me as you do, for an hypocrite. And no wonder if they did admire at it, that could not look into God’s secret intentions in it. Neither is it any wonder that I, who feel all this, complain, when every just man wonders at God’s proceedings with me.

*{at this}* That I being a godly man should be so much afflicted by God, and derided by men. Psal. 73. 13. Hab. 1. 2.

*{and the innocent}* He that is like clear water free not from all sin, but from hypocrisy and wickedness of life. So the word is used chap. 4. 7. & 9. 23.

*{shall stir up himself}* Or, *raise up himself, as* chap. 14. 12. He was asleep at first, but having better considered of the matter he will clear me, and join with me against mine enemies.

*{against the hypocrite}* See on chap. 8. 13. & 15. 34. Though the just man may wonder, when such sorrows befall him, yet having me for an example he will not join with the hypocrite, who prospers, but will keep the further off from his way, verse 9. Or, he will be very zealous to maintain his innocency against the hypocrite, who falsely accuses him. Or, *Because of the hypocrite*. Godly men not only are offended at their own miseries, but also at the prosperity of the wicked. But the former sense is best. Others take it, as if Job had said, I know that not only bad men, but also good men will wonder at me, and be offended at me for my miseries, as if I were an hypocrite. But this agrees neither with the words foregoing, nor following.

**V. 9. ***{The righteous also shall hold on his way, and he that hath clean hands shall be stronger and stronger}* Although a good man may be offended at my misery, and the hypocrite’s prosperity, yet not so as to cast off all piety, as if he measured God’s love by prosperity, and his hatred by adversity; contrary to Eccl. 9. 1, 2. but rather he will so much the more constantly go forward in virtue and godliness.

*{the righteous also}* He that truly fears God. The same with the innocent opposed to the hypocrite, verse 8.

*{shall hold on his way}* That is, go on in it to the end. Or, *Hold it fast*, and not go a step out of it. Or, *Lay fast hold on it*. So as he will not let it go. He will not give over the way of righteousness for all his miseries, and derision, nor follow ill ways for all the hypocrite’s prosperity. He shall be confirmed in piety notwithstanding this scandal: the word דרך or way signifies a distinct course from others, as the way from one town differs from the way to another. So in the literal sense, Deut. 3. 1. and in the figurative it is put for a distinct course of life, be it good or bad. Psal. 1. 6. Here it is taken for a course of piety.

*{and he that hath clean hands}* For the word Clean, see on chapter 11. 4. & 14. 4. For the word Hand, see on chap. 15. 23. The meaning is, he whose life is free from wicked ways, and sinful courses. Psal. 24. 4. Gen. 20. 5. for hands are put for actions in Scripture language.

*{shall be stronger and stronger}* Heb. *shall adde strength*. Isa. 40. 29, 31. Psal. 84. 7. He will be the more godly for his miseries, and other men’s deriding of him. Job shows hereby, that he had not cast off religion, and the fear of God, as Eliphaz had falsely accused him, Chap. 15. 4.

**V. 10. ***{But as for you all, do you return, and come now: for I cannot find one wise man among you}* Some knit these words to the words foregoing, and make the coherence thus, seeing I have proved that good men are subject to great troubles as well as bad, yield you to truth, and change your mind, and have a better opinion of me. Others join it to the words following, and make it to be a confutation of that hope of recovering his former estate, to which his friends persuaded him, and because he would not yield, construed him obliquely for a wicked man, chap. 15. 21. to which he answers, you are all mad men, that cannot see that nothing remains for a miserable man in my condition to hope for, but a grave. And this argument he prosecutes even to the end of the chapter. 1. he paints out with most elegant figures, his great and deplorable misery, verse 11, 12. and 2. shows that all hope of life was cut off from him, so that nothing was left him to think on but death.

*{but as for you all}* Heb. *But surely all they*. One person for another, which is frequent in Scripture, especially where men speak with troubled spirits, and so are apt to mistake persons, or doubt whether they should speak of or to them, that are before them. The like phrase is 1 King. 22. 28. Every one of you. Heb. *Of them*, so Mich. 1. 2.

*{do you return}* Chap. 6. 29. For the various significations of the Hebrew word, see on chap. 14. 13. Upon the word, *Be past*. Return from your false opinions, whereby you condemn me for an hypocrite, because of my sufferings: and give me vain hopes of restitution in this world. Others take it for a challenge, as if Job seeing them bustling as if they would confute him, should bid them do it, if they can. But this agrees not with what follows. For Job goes not on to satisfy them, much less tarries to see what they would say, but complains of his own misery.

*{come now}* Come hearken to me, and learn of me. This interpretation suits well with the words following.

*{for}* Heb. *and*. But it is often used to infer a reason of what went before, and is translated, *For*, as chap. 11. 4. So here you had need come to me to learn, for you all want wisdom.

*{I cannot find}* Or, *I shall not finde*. The future is translated, can, chap. 8. 11. the word מצא signifies, 1. finding without seeking, or finding by accident, as we call it, as Isa. 65. 1. So did Saul find Christ, Act. 9. 1. 2. finding by seeking or inquiry. Isa. 55. 6. Here it is used in the second sense. Job had observed all their speeches, but could not find any true wisdom in them.

*{one wise man among you}* You think yourselves wise and me foolish, but the wisest of you all may learn of me, 1 Cor. 5. 6.

**V. 11. ***{My days are past, my purposes are broken off, even the thoughts of my heart}* No marvel, if Job return unto his complaints, for his misery was so great, it would not permit him to dispute the matter any longer; yet he sets out his misery here but briefly in two verses, having set it out more largely before in the sixteenth chapter.

*{My days}* The time of my prosperity, or the days of my life. For the word, see on chap. 14. 14.

*{are past}* See on chap. 15. 19. It is in vain for me to hope for prosperity, those days are done with me, and the days of my life also well-nigh at an end, chap. 7. 6. & 9. 25.

*{my purposes}* My intentions of continuing in, or increasing my prosperity: or of using means of living longer. The word for the most part is used for the purposes of evil, but it is not likely, that Job would so charge himself, where he goes about to clear himself against his friends.

*{are broken off}* Or *plucked up*, as trees by the roots, they are clean destroyed, have no hope to effect them.

*{even the thoughts}* Heb. *the possessions*. Those thoughts wherewith my heart was possessed of the restoration of my prosperity in regard of my integrity, are now clean cut off. I see death approaching, which will put a period to them all, Psal. 146. 4. His sorrows were so great, that although he did endeavor to keep some hopes of life and prosperity in him, (as men naturally are willing to do even at the point of death) yet he could not nourish any such imagination within him.

*{of my heart}* See on chap. 15. 12.

**V. 12. ***{They change the night into day: the light is short because of darkness}* He shows the reason why he had no hope left of future prosperity, because he was worn with sorrow and pain, which gave him little or no rest day or night.

*{They}* My thoughts which perpetually trouble me, or my pains, which lie upon me without intermission, chap. 7. 3, 4, 13. or my friends which do so provoke me with bitter words, that I cannot sleep, verse 2. And then he speaks not to them, but rather complains to God of them speaking in the third person, they change &c.

*{change}* Heb. *put*. They keep me awake in the night, so that I cannot sleep, and so make it as day unto me.

*{the night}* It is given for man to rest, but to me it is as a day of labor.

*{into day}* See on chap. 14. 14.

*{The light}* The Hebrew word אור signifies, 1. the visible light, we behold with our eyes, and whereby we see all things else, Gen. 1. 3. 2. the sun, the most lightsome body in the world, and the eye of it, Job 31. 26. 3. the morning when the light first appears, Neh. 8. 3. 4. deliverance out of adversity, or prosperity or joy, Psal. 97. 11. 5. favor or grace, Prov. 16. 15. Here it may be taken in the second sense for the sun, which gives light in the day.

*{is short}* Heb. *neer*. If I have any ease or comfort in the day, it is long ere it comes, and the sun soon goes down upon it, see chap. 20. 5. there the word is so used.

*{because of darkness}* See on chap. 15. 22, 23. The night returning brings my pain again, and with the light of the day takes away all that little comfort the evening afforded me.

**V. 13. ***{If I wait, the grave is mine house: I have made my bed in the darkness}* In this verse and the next he shows, that if he would force himself to hope for life and restitution, he could not do it. And does as it were solemnly take his leave of his house and costly furniture, which he had enjoyed, as one ready now to dwell in the dust.

*{if}* If there be any hope left in me, it is at the most but of quietness after death in the grave. Or, *though*, so the word is used, chap. 9. 15. & 16. 6. If I should have any hope of longer life, or renewed prosperity, I should be deceived. Both I and my hopes must be suddenly lodged in the grave.

*{I wait}* Expect comfort promised by my friends upon my repentance, chap. 8. 6. & 11. 15, 16, 17.

*{the grave}* See on chap. 7. 9. What can a dying man wait for but a grave? see on verse 1.

*{is mine house}* Where I may rest, as men coming from their labors abroad may rest at home. Or, where I must abide longer than I have done in this world. The grave is man’s last house. He alludes to their words before, chap. 5. 24. This is the stately house you promised me, it is nothing but a dark hole in the earth. For the Hebrew word, and various uses of it, see on chap. 15. 28.

*{I have made my bed}* I prepare for my death, as a man makes his bed against night, I look for no rest here, in the grave I shall sleep quietly after my weary journey in this world, Isa. 57. 2.

*{In the darkness}* See on chap. 15. 22, 23. My windows will be shut up in the grave. There I may sleep quietly in the dark. Chap. 10. 21, 22.

**V. 14. ***{I have said to corruption, Thou art my father, to the worm; Thou art my mother, and my sister}* As in the former verse he had taken his leave of his house, so here to show how far he was from any hope of restitution, and what little account he made of all he had left in the world, he takes his leave of all his kindred, and as it were courts corruption, and the worms with terms of kindred, that they might be willing to receive him.

*{I have said}* Heb. *cried*, or *called*. For the word see on chap. 13. 22. I am certain, that I shall shortly putrefy in the grave.

*{to corruption}* Or, *to the pit*. Psal. 88. 4, 5. & 16. 10. They that go into the grave use to putrefy there.

*{Thou art my father}* I am a son of corruption, I must die, and molder away. Or corruption will give me ease of my pain like a tender father. For the word Father, see on chap. 15. 10.

*{to the worm}* Worms breed out of dead carcasses, and devour them, and therefore are fitly joined to corruption.

*{Thou art my mother}* Of kin to me. I shall be vile like you. Corruption and worms, that do as it were keep house in the grave, will receive me as a son of death, and then I shall be as it were at home with my friends and kindred eased of all my pain, which now lies so sore upon me. The Hebrew word אם is taken 1. for a natural mother, as Prov. 1. 8. 2. for the dam of a brute beast or bird, as Deut. 22. 6. 3. for a chief city, out of which spring villages, and are subject to it as to a mother, 2 Sam. 20. 19. 4. For the parting of a way, which like a mother brings forth several ways, as so many daughters, Ezek. 21. 21. Here it is taken in the first sense for a natural mother: yet with a metaphor. The worm would abide with him, as a mother with her children.

*{and my sister}* He mentions not brethren, because sisters are more at home, more tender, and more attendant on their sick brethren, than brethren are. Tamar is desired by Amnon to tend on him in his sickness, and not Absalom, 2 Sam. 13. 5, 6.

**V. 15. ***{And where is now my hope? as for my hope, who shall see it?}* Why do you then O Eliphaz delude me a wretched man, as it were already in the shadow of death, with hope of great prosperity, and account me a wicked man, because I will not believe it? I may rather admire at your folly, who would lift me up, and feed me with a vain hope against the course of nature, and God’s disposition of things. You shall soon see all those hopes go with me into the grave.

*{And where is now my hope}* Or, *And where then will my hope be?* It will be nowhere, when I shall be laid in the grave. The Hebrew is very emphatical, *And where, where is my hope?* I am sure nobody can tell where. Much like that trembling speech of Isaac, Gen. 27. 33. *Who, where is he, &c.* By hope here is meant the thing hoped for. How can I enjoy prosperity here after death? see on chap. 5. 16. & 7. 6. & 11. 18. & 14. 7.

*{as for my hope who shall see it?}* Who shall ever see me in that flourishing estate, which you promise me? who shall see that hope fulfilled? Not I, nor any man else. Thus low was Job brought past the Cape of Good Hope in his own apprehension, yet God raised him again, Chap. 42.

**V. 16. ***{They shall go down to the bars of the pit, when our rest together is in the dust.}* Here Job concludes what shall become of himself and his hopes of prosperity. They must be lodged together in the dust.

*{They}* My hopes, vers. 15. or My purposes and thoughts of prosperity, vers. 11. Those things you promise me, and my heart would be willing to hope for.

*{shall go down}* Shall vanish clean away, as things buried in the ground, for his wealth could not go with him to the grave. Or, it may be spoken ironically. Belike you think I shall be rich in the grave, who promise so much to me, for I have no hope to be rich in this world.

*{to the bars}* To the most inward parts, which uphold all the rest. I shall be utterly stripped of all prosperity, as if all my riches lay below me in the grave. The word is translated Strength, chap. 18. 13. And bars are the strength of a city, Deut. 3. 5. for they keep the gates fast, that the enemies cannot enter. And then the sense may be, Riches are as far from me, as if they were buried underground deep, where I could not come at them. See the like expression, though the Hebrew word differ, Jonas 2. 6.

*{of the pit}* See on chap. 7. 9.

*{when}* Heb. *If*. But it is also translated when, and sets out a time rather than a condition: see on chap. 14. 14. so it is translated, Psal. 63. 6.

*{our}* This word is added to explain the place. When you and I shall be lodged together in the grave. Neither you nor I shall ever see me prosper in this world. We shall both be dead before that time. Or, when I and my hopes shall both be gone out of the world.

*{rest}* Or *descent*. When we shall go into the grave, or lie quietly in it.

*{together}* One with another, Chapter 3. 17, 18, 19. & 30. 23, 24.

*{is in the dust}* Or, *shall be in the dust*. Which is likely to be quickly. The latter end of the verse may be thus read, *when I shall rest alone in the dust*. That is, when I being stripped of all my possessions shall lie solitary in the grave, chap. 1. 21. And that the word יחד is used for Alone, see on Ezr. 4. 3. It is so translated. *Against a man only*, Job 34. 29.

## CHAPTER 18

**Vers. 1. ***{Then answered Bildad the Shuhite and said,}* In this second conflict Bildad rather inveighs against Job in a sharp and angry oration, than disputes. He contends, that however Job go about to defend himself, yet it might appear by those judgments, which now lay on him, and which God did use to lay so heavily on none but ungodly men, that he and his fellows did not accuse Job without a cause. The parts of this chapter are two. First, a preface in the four first verses. 2. A witty description of the miseries of wicked men both in life, in death, and after death. He does no other thing than he did chap. 8. save only that he does more lively set out, and paint to the quick the miseries of ungodly men. But he does not speak anything as before of the reward of good men, or persuade Job to repentance; as if he threatened all these miseries to Job as unavoidable. Yet he falls somewhat from his first principles, as beaten from them by Job, who had clearly evinced, that in this world good men sometimes were punished, and bad men preferred; and therefore he minces the matter and pleads, that if good men be afflicted for a time, yet they shall flourish afterwards in this world; and if bad men prosper a while, yet they shall certainly come to misery here before they die. In the preface is set out, 1. the speaker in this verse. 2. the matter in the three verses following.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4. 1.

*{answered}* See on chap. 3. 2. & 4. 1. & 16. 1.

*{Bildad the Shuhite}* See on chap. 2. 11.

*{and said}* See on chap. 3. 2. & 4. 1. & 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{How long will it be, ere you make an end of words? mark, and afterwards we will speak.}* In the matter of this preface, Bildad charges Job with three great faults. 1. Loquacity, and too much talking, more than befitted him, in this verse. 2. Pride and arrogant contempt of others, in the third verse. 3. Fretfulness and murmuring against God, in the fourth verse.

*{How long will it be}* It is true that Job's speeches were longer than theirs, in regard of his great pressures, and their unjust censures. Yet he is not to be accounted tedious who in his just defense speaks much to the purpose: They rather were guilty of this sin, who speak the same things so often, and repeat things answered by Job before, to vex him. See the like phrase, chap. 8. 2.

*{ere you}* He speaks to Job in the plural number. Some conceive the reason to be, because he would include his fellows, Eliphaz and Zophar, as well as Job, accusing them of making too long speeches, and not understanding one another, whereby, they kept him from speaking, that thought he could better clear the matter. But he had no quarrel with his fellows, and he accounts them wronged by Job as well as himself, vers. 3. Either therefore he includes Job's friends who stood by, and by gestures and cheerful countenance might encourage him, as chap. 34. 2. or else, the plural number is put for the singular, which is not unusual in the Hebrew. As Mich. 1. 11. So he speaks of himself in the plural, in this verse. And afterwards we will speak.

*{make}* Heb. *put*.

*{an end}* Heb. *ends*. It seems that out of impatience Bildad cut off Job's speech before he was willing to make an end. See on chap. 8. 1.

*{of words}* Such as these that are nothing but words. He thought Job's words had no weight in them.

*{mark}* He conceives that Job did not rightly understand him, and his fellows, and therefore had need to attend better to them, and not take upon him to teach them, as chap. 17. 10. he seemed to undertake.

*{and afterwards we will speak}* When you are in a capacity to hear and learn, then I will bestow more pains to teach you.

**V. 3. ***{Wherefore are we counted as beasts, and reputed vile in your sight?}* You O Job not only answer rashly, and nothing to the purpose, but are grown so insolent, that you forget we are men, and make no more account of us, than of the beasts of the field. O my companions, had not we need to take notice of this injury, and to beat down Job's arrogancy?

*{Wherefore}* Show us a reason of this your bold assertion, and aspersion of us.

*{are we}* I and my fellows. If I would swallow mine own disgrace, yet I must not theirs.

*{counted}* By you O Job, See on chap. 13. 24.

*{as beasts}* He alludes to Job's speeches, who had sent them to the beasts to learn, chap. 12. 7. And pronounced them void of understanding, chap. 17. 4. and of wisdom, chap. 17. 10. What was this but to make them blockish and senseless like the beasts? But Bildad should rather have considered, what great pains lay on Job, and what provocations they had given him, and whether they had not carried themselves so, that they deserved such language. These thoughts would have taught him patience.

*{and reputed vile}* Heb. *polluted, or unclean*, and therefore fit to be rejected. Why do you O Job despise us as vile things, unfit to be looked upon by your delicate eyes? chap. 19. 15. Some think it to be an allusion to unclean beasts, which were not looked upon as fit for sacrifices, nor allowed for food under the law. It rather may have respect to beasts wallowing in the dirt, and growing filthy for want of wit to keep themselves clean, and for their filthiness loathed by men. For the word see on chap. 14. 4.

*{in your sight}* Heb. *in your eyes*. For the phrase see on chap. 15. 15. He means in Job's judgment or account, as chap. 19. 15. & 32. 1. Prov. 3. 7. Isa. 5. 21. Why he speaks to Job in the plural number, see on ver. 2.

**V. 4. ***{He teareth himself in his anger: shall the earth be forsaken for thee? and shall the rock be removed out of his place?}* He accuses Job here as one not only insolent over them, but injurious to God, and raging against him, as if he would have God to alter the whole course of nature to please him.

*{He teareth}* Some would have it to be one person for another, and read it, *O thou that tearest thy self*. See the like alteration of the person, chap. 2. 4. & 16. 7. & 30. 19, 20. Obad. vers. 3. But there is more elegancy in this change of the person. Bildad first looks upon his friends, and bids them take notice, how furiously Job carried himself. Do you not see how he rages? Then he turns himself to Job, and looks him in the face, and disdaining his speech to him goes on in the second person, *shall the earth be forsaken for thee?* He compares Job to a furious beast, tearing himself, as the beast does his prey, whereby he intimates Job's extreme vexing himself in his anger, as if he would tear himself to pieces: He takes occasion to charge him thus from Job's words, chap. 13. 14. See on chap. 16. 9. The word is used Gen. 37. 33.

*{himself}* Heb. *his soul*. For the word see on chap. 14. 22.

*{in his anger}* See on chap. 14. 13.

*{shall the earth}* See on chap. 9. 24.

*{be forsaken}* Of God. Should he give over governing it justly to please you? Or should it be forsaken of men? would you have God to destroy all men on the earth to humor you?

*{for thee}* For your sake, that you may have content, and no man may be left upon earth to speak against you for your wickedness.

*{and shall the rock}* Shall God change the order of his governing the world justly in punishing the wicked and prospering the godly, which is as firm and constant, and unmovable, as a rock? It may be he alludes to Job's speech, chap. 14. 18. though Job use the similitude of a rock there to another end.

*{be removed out of his place}* And carried into another place. This is impossible. So it is for you to clear yourself, unless you accuse God of injustice in his proceedings. Or it may be he accuses Job of unreasonableness, that could be content, that all the world might be put out of order, so he might enjoy his will.

**V. 5. ***{Yea the light of the wicked shall be put out, and the spark of his fire shall not shine.}* Now Bildad having finished his preface endeavors to make his cause good, and largely sets out the destruction of ungodly men, 1. by similitudes to vers. 27. 2. in plain terms, from thence to the end of the chapter.
