The similitudes are four. 1. from the light in vers. 5, 6. 2. from hawking and hunting, vers. 7, 8, 9, 10. 3. from forensical proceeding, or military; vers. 11, 12, 13, 14, 15. 4. from husbandry, vers. 16. For the similitude from light. Notwithstanding your protestations of your innocency O Job, you are wicked, for God puts out your light, as he uses to do with ungodly men.

*{Yea}* See on chap. 16. 9. on the word, *Also*. For the Hebrew word is the same. Here it may be used by way of augmentation, as if he had said to Job, You feel God's hand heavy on you, and you fret and fume, vers. 4. but it will go worse with you yet, for the light of the wicked, such as you are, shall be put out.

*{the light}* For the word see on chap. 17. 12. By light here is meant whatsoever outwardly shines and seems glorious in the eyes of men, as wealth, honor, friends. For as light cheers our spirits, directs us to work or walk, and makes us to be looked upon by others, so does prosperity. So is Light used, chap. 22. 28. & 30. 26. & 38. 15. Psal. 97. 11. Prov. 13. 9. Isa. 59. 9. Esth. 8. 16. Job 11. 17.

*{of the wicked}* See on chap. 3. 17.

*{shall be put out}* Though God suffer them to prosper for a time, yet he will bring them to misery in the end. Their prosperity shall fail them, as a snuff of candle goes out and leaves a stink behind it. Men did not so much admire them in their prosperity, but they shall as much contemn them in their adversity. He uses many several words in this verse, and the next to the same purpose, to show that their prosperity shall suddenly and unexpectedly be taken away, so that no part of it shall appear any more, which will be a great wonder to the beholders, as if they should see the Sun go down at noon.

*{and the spark}* He does not say the flame, but the spark, intimating a total destruction of their prosperity, as a fire is quite out, when not so much as one spark appears. 2 Sam. 14. 7.

*{of his fire}* The word אש is derived from a word that signifies to be strong or mighty, for fire has a great power to burn, heat and consume. It signifies 1. literally the fire, that we use to warm us. Job 16. 2. it is put for war, which devours and sets fire on all where it comes, Psal. 78. 62, 63. 3. for a strong burning wind that parches grass and corn, yea the very stubble, Joel 2. 5. 4. for trial of God's people by afflictions, which tries them, as fire tries gold, Zech. 13. 9. Psal. 66. 12. 5. for prosperity, which warms and comforts men like fire, and makes them shine before others. So in this place. For what he called light before, now he calls fire. Of his fire. That is, of every wicked man's. Before he spoke of them in general, in the plural number, now in the singular, as if he pointed to every particular man, and intended to show, that not one wicked man's prosperity shall continue.

*{shall not shine}* Sparks do fly up quickly, but do not shine, they are soon extinguished: so is the prosperity of the wicked. The word may also import an utter extinguishing of it, so that they shall never prosper again. No morning of prosperity shall come after the night of their adversity. For the word sometimes imports the breaking forth of the morning light, so the word is used. Isa. 59. 9.

**V. 6. ***{The light shall be darke, his tabernacle, and his candle shall be put out with him.}* Both he, himself, and all his house shall fall out of this light of comfort, into extreme darkness of misery, by reason that his light shall be suddenly turned into darkness, and his beauty and brightness in a moment shall be turned into deformity and misery.

*{The light}* See on vers. 5.

*{shall be darke}* Or, *darkened*. It shall be clean taken away, and darkness come instead of it. For the word see on chap. 15. 21, 23.

*{in his tabernacle}* There shall no prosperity at all be left in his family. He and all his shall come to ruin. The glory of his family shall be obscured. For the word see on chap. 5. 14. & 11. 14.

*{and his candle}* Or, *lamp*. His prosperity called light, and fire verse 5. As the light of the Sun, so the light of a candle is used to set out prosperity, chap. 29. 3. 2 King. 8. 19. Psal. 18. 28.

*{shall be put out with him}* He shall lose his prosperity and his life both together. For the word, Put out, see on vers. 5. Or, *His candle which is with him shall be put out*: so על is used, chap. 38. 32. See the like phrase, 1 Cor. 15. 10. His prosperity shall cease before he die. Or, *Above him*. For lights in feasts were wont to be set on high, that they might give the more light. Or, *His candle in it*. That is, in his tabernacle. All his riches and goods in his house shall be taken away; so this particle is used for In, Psal. 62. 8.

**V. 7. ***{The steps of his strength shall be straitened, and his own counsel shall cast him down.}* Now he proceeds to the second similitude, wherein he compares ungodly men to wild beasts and birds, for whom nets and snares are set everywhere, and most of all where they least suspect them; which makes them often run into snares unawares, neither can their strength or wittiness do them any good, when they are taken; He also sets out the many several ways that God has to ruin ungodly men by the several devices, hunters and fowlers have to catch beasts and birds, as by nets, snares, gins, traps. And as they scare birds and beasts, and make them use their own legs and wings to bring them into snares, so does God bring down wicked men by their own counsels and actions. The sum of this verse is to show, that whereas before in his prosperity nothing was so difficult, but a wicked man durst take it in hand, and make sure account to bring it to pass by his strength, now when want and penury is come upon him, his heart fails him, and he is brought into straits, and while he seeks by policy to free himself, he hastens his own destruction.

*{The steps}* The devices of his mind, or actions which he goes about, which are so many steps made to go forward in a way: so it is interpreted in the end of the verse.

*{of his strength}* Those strong steps of subtle counsels, and state actions, which he made in his prosperity to accomplish his ends. The Hebrew word און signifies, 1. strength, as Gen. 39. 3. 2. riches which are often gotten by labor and strength. We call it substance, Hos. 12. 8. 3. iniquity, for men often wrong, and spoil others by the abuse of their strength, Job 21. 19. 4. vanity, for vain is the strength of man, Jer. 4. 14. 5. lamentation and sorrow, Deut. 26. 14. for such is man's strength. Psal. 90. 10. Here it is taken in the first sense for such steps, as strong men use to take.

*{shall be straitened}* Strong steps make quick dispatch, except they want room, and be hindered by the narrowness of the way. The wicked man cannot carry all before him in his adversity, as he did in his prosperity, he wants then counsel and courage to oppress others or escape himself. He is like a bird or beast in a snare, he cannot go at liberty as before. The Hebrew word יצר signifies, 1. to be straitened, as Prov. 4. 12. 2. to frame or fashion a thing, which is often done by straitening and pressing down the matter, whereof it is made, Gen. 2. 7. 3. framing in the mind, or imagining, for thoughts are framed by the heart, as works by the hand, Jer. 18. 11. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{and his own counsel}* His own devices and those counsels which his own head gives him, shall ruin him. Prov. 1. 31. & 14. 14.

*{shall cast him downe}* Into those miseries into which he is fallen. Or, *shall cast him away*. That is, his former policies shall fail him, his wit is lost within his wealth, and his brains with his substance.

**V. 8. ***{For he is cast into a net by his own feet, and he walketh upon a snare.}* This verse is an explication or rather an amplification of the former, and shows by what means his steps were straitened, in that not only he has brought himself into a strait place, but also is taken in a net there by his own foolishness, so that he cannot get out.

*{For}* Why is he in such straits? Because he has brought himself into them.

*{He is cast}* For the word see on chap. 14. 20. Here it signifies sending forth or casting away. He is like a beast hunted into a net.

*{into a net}* The word רשת is taken, 1. literally for an engine to catch birds, or beasts, or fishes, Hos. 5. 1. Prov. 1. 17. 2. For network, Exod. 27. 4. 3. for politic devices to entrap men, Psal. 9. 15. 4. for a condition of misery, out of which there is no getting, Psal. 66. 11. So it is used here.

*{by his own feet}* That is, being taken in it by his own feet. He is not only cast into that irrecoverable misery by God's secret providence overruling all things, but of his own accord runs into it, though unwillingly. For one verb thus understood or included in another, see chap. 15. 8. & 16. 11. 1 Sam. 7. 2. Isa. 38. 14, 17, 21. Heb. *in his own feet*. But the particle ב is oft used for an instrument, Psal. 33. 6. so that here is both the chief cause or inflicter of his misery included, and likewise the instrument, God and the wicked man himself. His feet here are taken for his ways or courses by a figure of the cause for the effect, and a metaphor. The meaning is, he shall by God's just judgment bring himself into inextricable difficulties.

*{and he walketh}* See on chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Passeth*. Here it signifies a being in continual danger, as a beast, that has all his way spread with nets to catch him, that if he escape one, is sure to be taken in another.

*{upon a snare}* Broughton reads it, *a platted gin*. And fitly, for it comes from a word, that signifies platting or wreathing things together, as ropes are twisted, and boughs of trees grow one within another. So nets are made, and snares. Walking upon nets and snares, is like walking upon coals. A proverb to set out perpetual danger.

**V. 9. ***{The gin shall take him by the heel, and the robber shall prevail against him}* He shall not only be cast into the net, but also be taken in it by the heel, and that so fast, that there shall be no getting out no more than a weak man can get out of the hands of a thief, that is too strong for him.

*{The gin shall take him}* Lay fast hold on him so that he cannot escape, Eccl. 9. 12. Cant. 3. 4.

*{by the heel}* עקב comes from a word that signifies, to be crooked, and signifies, 1. the heel which is crooked about, Gen. 25. 26. 2. the end of a thing, for the heel is at the lower end of the body, Psal. 119. 33. 3. the reward of an action, good or bad, Psal. 19. 11. Here it is taken in the first sense. Others read it, *He shall lay hold on the gin with his heel*. That is, he shall undo himself by his own oversight.

*{and the robber}* See on chap. 5. 5. Yet here may be meant the hunter, that lays the snares to catch the wild beast.

*{shall prevail}* He shall be too strong for him, and by his might shall overcome him. Here he shows what snares he meant. The wicked man had by fraud oppressed others, and God would give him over to robbers to be spoiled by them.

*{against him}* He shall come against him violently, and set upon him like an enemy, and utterly destroy him. Or, *upon him*. Or, *over him*. He shall get a complete victory over him, and take away all he has gotten indirectly from others, Mat. 12. 29.

**V. 10. ***{The snare is laid for him in the ground, and a trap for him in the way}* As hunters and fowlers sometimes make snares of cords to catch birds or beasts by the legs, and sometimes make pitfalls in the ground for them to fall into, so the wicked man wherever he walks, shall find snares and traps laid to catch him in every place.

*{the snare}* Heb. *His rope*. To wit, wherewith he shall be taken. The word חבל signifies, 1. a rope or cord wherewith things are tied, Esth. 1. 6. 2. a snare, whereby beasts or birds are taken, Psal. 104. 4. 3. a measuring cord, or line whereby land is measured, Amos 7. 17. 4. a man's portion of land, that falls to him by such measuring, or otherwise, Psal. 16. 5, 6. 5. grief or pain, whereby men or women are taken and tied up as it were with a cord, Isa. 66. 7. 6. corruption or destruction, which binds men up like a cord, so that there is no getting out of it, Mich. 2. 10. 7. a company of men knit together as with a cord. 1 Sam. 10. 5. Here it is taken in the second sense.

*{is laid for him}* Heb. *hidden*. If the snare were to be seen the birds would avoid it. The meaning is, that God has many secret ways to bring down ungodly men. An allusion to fowlers, who cover their snares, and pits with boughs or boards, lest the bird should see them and escape. Psal. 194. 5.

*{in the ground}* See on chap. 9. 24.

*{and a trap for him}* Heb. *his trap*. That is, a trap laid to catch him. So the word imports, for it comes from a word that signifies to take towns and cities.

*{in the way}* Or, *by the path*. For על, In, see on vers. 6. Some think the Hebrew word here used signifies a path in woods where snares use to be laid to catch wild beasts, that walk there. However it is the custom of hunters and fowlers to lay snares in or near ways, where the beasts or birds use to pass, as adders & serpents use to lie in paths to sting passengers, Gen. 49. 17. The wicked man's ways here are his actions and courses of life, out of which occasion is taken by others to undo him.

**V. 11. ***{Terrors shall make him afraid on every side, and shall drive him to his feet}* Now follows an elegant similitude taken from guilty persons in a state, that are in danger of a public trial for great offenses. In such a condition are wicked men, though in prosperity at present. And 1. he sets out the terrors they feel in their life, vers. 11, 12. 2. their horrible death, vers. 13, 14. 3. the destruction of their houses after their death, vers. 15. like traitors, who are clapped up in prison, racked there, put to a shameful and painful death, and their houses burnt or pulled down, and so to remain as a perpetual brand of infamy to their family. In all this Bildad strikes closely at Job as at a wicked man, who had before complained of his terrors and affrightments, chap. 7. 14. of his goods taken away, and house left desolate, chap. 16. 7, 11. and of other like miseries in many places. The sum of the verse is, that as the wicked man shall have many imminent threatening dangers abroad, so he must look for many terrors within, so that he shall not think himself anywhere safe, but ready to betake himself to flight, if he could tell which way to escape.

*{Terrors}* Terrors of conscience, or fears of mind arising from his present troubles, which have seized, and make him to fear greater mischiefs.

*{shall make afraid}* Chap. 15. 21, see there.

*{On every side}* Heb. *round*. Out of every place he shall fear trouble, as if an army of fears were round about him, Jer. 6. 20. & 25. 3. & 46. 5. & 49. 29.

*{and shall drive him}* Heb. *scatter him*.

*{to his feet}* He shall think himself safe in no place, but betake himself to his legs to fly from place to place for safety. He shall not trust to others to hide him, but use his own legs to get away.

**V. 12. ***{His strength shall be hunger-bitten, and destruction shall be ready at his side}* Before he had showed what outward troubles and inward fears of mind should lay hold on the wicked man, now he sets down what he must feel in his body.

*{His strength}* See on vers. 7.

*{shall be hunger-bitten}* He that had had abundance and fared daintily, and was strong before, now God has blown upon his estate shall be so poor, that his bodily strength shall decay for want of food, chap. 15. 23. 1 Sam. 2. 5, 36.

*{and destruction}* The word literally signifies a cloud, that darkens the sky, and suddenly dissolves, and comes down in an unlooked for tempest. So shall sudden destruction daunt, & ruin wicked men, Pro. 6. 16.

*{shall be ready}* Some take it for strong and stable, so prepared that he cannot prevent it. But it is better translated Ready. That is, near at hand, as appears by the word following.

*{at his side}* The word צלע signifies, 1. a rib of a man or woman. Gen. 2. 22. 2. because man's rib is in his side, it signifies the side of anything, or a place near it, 2 Sam. 16. 13. 3. it signifies the beams of a house, which join it together, as ribs do the body, 1 King. 7. 3. 4. it signifies a chamber set at the outside of the house, as many were about the Temple, 1 King. 6. 8. Here it is taken in the second sense. And it intimates that his destruction is as near him, as one that stands close by his side. See the like phrase, Psal. 91. 7. but there it signifies near in place, here near in time. It will come suddenly.

**V. 13. ***{It shall devour the strength of his skin: even the first born of death shall devour his strength}* From the sufferings of the wicked man in his life he now goes on to show the harshness of his death.

*{It}* Destruction spoken of vers. 12. shall consume him. Or, *the first born of death*, as in the words following. And so the Dutch translation reads it. *The first born of death shall devour, &c.* So the antecedent is taken out of the words following, as Psal. 87. 1, 2.

*{shall devour}* See on chap. 15. 34. on the word, *Consume*.

*{the strength}* Heb. *the bars*. See on chap. 17. 16.

*{of the skin}* Some by the bars or strength of his skin understand the flesh, which upholds his skin. Others the bones which uphold both flesh and skin, and carry it. Others translate it *branches*. And some of them understand it of the members that are under the skin, that shoot like branches. And some, of his children, which are as it were branches of his body. It is most like to be meant of the destruction of his flesh or bones.

*{the first-born of death}* The word בכור signifies, 1. the firstborn, as Gen. 27. 32. 2. a prince, or ruler, because the firstborn of the family had the rule at first, Psal. 89. 27. 3. one that is dear and precious to us, as a firstborn son, Exod. 4. 22. 3. one that is elder than another, because the firstborn is the eldest, Gen. 43. 33. Some understand by the firstborn of death a violent death, which seizes upon all a man's members, as in the death of a wild beast. For the firstborn was the father's strength, Gen. 49. 3. so the firstborn of the poor is the poorest, Isa. 14. 30. So the firstborn of death is a most terrible death, that carries away the principality from other kinds of deaths, as the firstborn did from his brethren.

*{Shall devour his strength}* See before on this verse for both these words.

**V. 14. ***{His confidence shall be rooted out of his tabernacle, and it shall bring him to the King of terrors.}* In all this mischief which befalls him, he shall not know whither to go for help, whatsoever in his house he trusted in, shall be taken away, and he shall find no way to escape a terrible death.

*{his confidence}* See on chap. 8. 14. & 11. 20. Prov. 10. 28. All hope and means of reestablishing him in his former estate shall be taken away, as health, strength, wealth, and whatever else he trusted in in his prosperity. Or, he shall have nothing left with him, which may give him any assurance of continuance in his house.

*{Shall be rooted out}* There shall nothing remain on which he may trust: He can see no safe abiding in his dwelling.

*{of his tabernacle}* See on chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

*{and it shall bring him}* Heb. *shall make him go*. Or, *this shall bring him*. To wit, the failing of his confidence or despair, in that he sees himself stripped of all comforts, on which he leaned before. Or, *thou shalt bring him*. As if Bildad turned his speech aside to to God; and lest the wicked man in his height of prosperity should think, that no man could pull him down, tells him that God would do it. Or impersonally, *He shall be brought*. See on chap. [42…]

*{to the King of terrors}* To death, which is more terrible to flesh and blood, than anything else. To the greatest of all terrors that might rule over all the rest. As the firstborn of death for the most terrible death, vers. 13. For terrors see on vers. 11.

**V. 15. ***{It shall dwell in his tabernacle, because it is none of his; brimstone shall be scattered upon his habitation}* He now comes to set out the ruin of his house, signifying, that Job his wickedness would not only destroy him, but his habitation also.

*{It}* Terror or despair spoken of vers. 14. There shall be no hope, that any shall dwell in his house any more; except terror and desolation: for it shall be destroyed by God's judgment, as follows in the text.

*{shall dwell}* Constantly abide there.

*{in his tabernacle}* See on chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

*{because it is none of his}* Heb. *Not his*. because he got the money, with which he built it, by fraud and indirect means, therefore God will turn him out of doors, and let nothing but terror dwell there. This is added emphatically by way of correction. Did I call it his tabernacle? Alas I was mistaken; it is none of his, it was built with other men's goods. Others understand it thus, It is now no longer his, for death the King of terrors has put him out of possession.

*{brimstone}* An allusion to the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah, Gen. 19. and to the custom of punishing men for treason, when not only the traitors themselves are put to a cruel death, but also their houses are destroyed for a note of infamy. Hereby is intended some exemplary judgment of God, that should light upon the ungodly man, Deut. 29. 23. Isa. 34. 9. He may allude also to the destruction of Job's sheep and shepherds by fire from heaven, chap. 1. 16. or to thunderbolts which smell like brimstone.

*{shall be scattered}* Spread abroad as seed is cast upon the ground.

*{upon his habitation}* Upon his dwelling house, and all that belongs to it.

**V. 16. ***{His roots shall be dried up beneath, and above shall his branch be cut off}* This is the last similitude where Bildad sets out the destruction of ungodly men by a similitude taken from country affairs. He had before compared a wicked man to a flourishing tree rooted fast in the earth and spreading his branches abroad, Chapter 8. 16, 17. now by the same similitude he sets out his destruction. As a tree, when the root withers, decays in the branches also, so shall this ungodly man be utterly destroyed both root and branch, Mar. 11. 20. Such similitudes from trees are frequent in Scripture, Job 29. 19. Isa. 5. 24. Amos 2. 9. The intent is to show, that mischief will befall the ungodly man on every side, as a tree whose roots are withered and branches cut off, and it left dead and naked.

*{His roots}* See on chap. 5. 3. & 14. 8. & 8. 17.

*{shall be dried up}* Wither for want of moisture.

*{beneath}* Within the bowels of the earth.

*{and above}* Above ground, or upon the top of the tree.

*{shall his branch}* See on chap. 14. 9. on the word *Boughs*. Some understand his children by the branch, but then his parents must be the roots, which is not here intended. But by his roots may be meant all his secret wealth and store, which is unknown to the world, as roots of trees lie unseen in the ground, and by his branch may be meant his outward pomp, which is seen by all men as the branches of trees, and is upheld by his inward store, as a tree by the roots. All must be lost within doors and without.

*{be cut off}* Taken away as branches cut off from trees. The word signifies circumcising or cutting round. He that lops the branches cuts round, till the bough fall down. So will God cut off all the hold of ungodly men, and make them fall with their own weight, See on chap. 14. 2. on the word, *cut down*.

**V. 17. ***{His remembrance shall perish from the earth, and he shall have no name in the street}* What he had before spoken allegorically of the destruction of ungodly men, now he sets out in plain terms to the end of the chapter, that Job might not misunderstand him. Also he does particularly set out those things which follow after the wicked man's death. The first whereof is ignominy, and contempt, set out in this verse. After many perils and calamities in this world, and a miserable death, his name shall be buried with him. And he that was so much magnified while he lived, shall not be spoken of unless it be in disgrace after his death, Psal. 49. 18, 19. As a tree when root and branch is gone, is clean forgotten, and no man remembers where it grew, Psal. 109. 13. Prov. 10. 7.

*{His remembrance}* See on Job 13. 12.

*{shall perish}* There shall be no remembrance of him. No man shall speak of him. He shall quite fall out of the memories of men. So the day is said to perish, when it is not, chap. 3. 3. and expectation perishes, when men have nothing to expect, Prov. 10. 28.

*{from the earth}* The men of the earth shall speak of him no more: see on chap. 9. 24.

*{and he shall have no name}* Not so much as once be spoken of or named. Or, *no credit of renown*. So men of name in the Hebrew are men of renown, in the translation, Gen. 6. 4. He shall have no honorable name left, whereby posterity shall remember him. His evil deeds, and God's judgments on him have blotted it clean out.

*{in the street}* Heb. *upon the face of the street*. For the word Face see on chap. 14. 20. For the word חוץ street, it signifies 1. without, or abroad, Lev. 18. 9. 2. the streets of a city, which are without the houses, Jer. 37. 21. 3. country villages and fields which are out of the walled towns, and lie open to all comers and dangers, Prov. 1. 20. Gen. 19. 16. Numb. 35. 4. Men use to meet together in the streets, and there to talk of all occurrences. But no man shall ever make mention of this wicked wretch there, who is fallen out of the memory of all men living.

**V. 18. ***{He shall be driven from light into darkness, and chased out of the world}* Now follows the revenge, that God, and men oppressed by this wicked wretch will take of him: they will never leave him, till they have rooted him clean out of the world.

*{He shall be driven}* Heb. *they shall drive him*. It may be taken impersonally, as chap. 3. 3. see there; yet it may be with respect to the persons wronged by him. They shall find a time to be revenged on him, and never leave him, till they have taken away his life. So the Dutch translation reads it, *men shall drive him*.

*{from light into darkness}* Not literally from day to night, nor from prosperity to adversity, not from joy to sorrow, as the words sometimes are taken, but from life to death, as appears by the words following. See on vers. 5. chap. 17. 12, 13. & 15. 22, 23. Isa. 8. 22.

*{and chased}* Heb. *and chase him*. As dogs follow the wild boar till they kill him, so such as have been undone by him shall pursue him to death, Psal. 140. 11.

*{Out of the world}* Not only out of his country, but out of all the habitable world. He shall no more converse with men here upon earth.

**V. 19. ***{He shall neither have son nor nephew among his people, nor any remaining in his dwellings,}* He goes on and shows the misery of this ungodly man, in that not only himself shall be chased out of the world, but his posterity also. He labored to get riches for them, but none of them shall enjoy it. It is likely that he girds at Job, and obliquely, though not in direct terms, objects to him the loss of his children by God's extraordinary judgment, chap. 1. 19. which Eliphaz also had objected to him, chap. 5. 4. And Bildad himself more plainly, chap. 8. 4.

*{he shall neither have son nor nephew}* None of his posterity shall inherit his possessions. See Gen. 21. 23. Isa. 14. 22. It may be he might have children or nephews, but they should never enjoy what he had gotten, as Jer. 22. 30. so it follows in this verse, They shall have no place among his people, nor have their father's dwelling, as children use to have.

*{among his people}* In his country or city.

*{nor any remaining}* Either the meaning is that all his family and kindred should be destroyed together with him, or else that they should live in a poor fashion, not in such a stately house, and enjoying such great means as he did. The word properly signifies one that is left in war, and not destroyed by the sword. Such as were oppressed by him shall so hate him, that they shall seek to destroy him, and all his family: see on vers. 19. Jer. 42. 17. Lam. 1. 22.

*{in his dwellings}* Heb. *in his abidings*. The word rather signifies a place wherein strangers abide for a time, than houses of men's own. The wicked man thought he had got a sure habitation for himself and his: but it was but as an inn to him in his pilgrimage, and his posterity shall never enjoy it.

**V. 20. ***{They that come after him, shall be astonied at his day, as they that went before were affrighted.}* The calamity of that last day, when the wicked man shall die, shall be so terrible, that it shall be full of horror and admiration, not only to those that shall see it with their eyes, but to those also, that in after times shall hear the relation of it.

*{They that come after him}* Such as knew him not, but hear by others the relation of his fearful fall.

*{shall be astonied}* See on chap. chap. 17. 8. It signifies such an astonishment, as uses to possess passers by, when some pleasant place is laid utterly waste, or when heaps of dead carcasses lie on the ground after a great battle. Jer. 19. 3, 8. Lev. 26. 32. So terrible shall be the destruction of this wicked man, and his whole family, that even the ages that come after, when they hear of it, shall stand aghast, as if they beheld the dirty ruins of some once beautiful city.

*{at his day}* See on chap. 14. 14. Here is meant the day of the destruction of the ungodly man: or rather the destruction itself falling out on that day, which should be so great that it should be as it were yearly observed by recapitulation of God's hand upon this wicked wretch, and that from generation to generation. For the phrase, see Obad. vers. 12. Psal. 137. 5, 37. 13. Ezek. 21. 25.

*{as}* Heb. *and*, but it is often translated as, and is a note of similitude: so Job 34. 3.

*{they that went before}* Who lived before these men, that only heard of it. That is, such as lived at the same time, when this wicked man was destroyed, and saw his terrible destruction.

*{were affrighted}* Heb. *laid hold on horrour*. The word signifies such a fright as makes a man's hair stand an end, Ezek. 27. 35. & 32. 10. Job 4. 15. The meaning is by inversion of the words, that horror has taken hold on them, Isa. 13. 8. & 21. 3.

**V. 21. ***{Surely such are the dwellings of the wicked, and this is the place of him, that knoweth not God.}* He concludes all with threatening a certain ruin and destruction of the habitation of every wicked man, and sets down the cause of it, because they have no acquaintance with God, and therefore can look for no protection from him.

*{Surely}* This you may make account of: I will say no more, but leave you to observe whether it be so or no.

*{such}* Heb. *these*. Which is more emphatical. As if he had set them out so plainly, that men might see them falling with their eyes.

*{are the dwellings}* Thus God will deal with the dwellings of ungodly men. They think themselves safe in their sumptuous houses; but they shall be destroyed. This shall be the end of that once flourishing palace of the wicked man.

*{of the wicked}* He still speaks in the singular number, as if he had a fling at Job, and would have him apply all this to himself.

*{and this is the place}* This is the house. So a place is taken Exod. 18. 23. Job 8. 18. you see what is become of his house. It is utterly ruined. And so it answers fitly to the beginning of the verse. Or, This is the estate and condition of the ungodly man.

*{of him that knoweth not God}* A description of a wicked man. One that has no acquaintance with God, nor does not worship him according to his will. Exod. 5. 2. Judg. 2. 10. Psal. 79. 6. 2 Thess. 1. 8. Or, *Of him who doth not approve of God*. So the word is used, Psal. 1. 6. But the former reading is the better, because it answers best to the beginning of the verse. And expounds what is meant by the wicked man whose habitation should be utterly destroyed, namely such an one, as had no acquaintance with God at all. For the word God see on Chap. 8. 3, 5. Thus ends Bildad's second furious charge against Job with as little success as his former, as will appear, when we have considered Job's answer in the following chapter.

## CHAPTER 19

**Vers. 1. ***{Then Job answered and said}* In this chapter Job moves his friends to pity him with such passionate expressions as might move stony hearts. He acknowledges, that not only those miseries Bildad spoke of, but also many others lay sore upon him. And that they came not by chance, but by God's appointment. Yet he shows, that he should not therefore be accounted a wicked man, nor be so reproachfully dealt withal by his friends, but rather be pitied so much the more, by how much the greater his affliction was. And hoping to find but little comfort from his friends, he plainly declares, that although he looked for no restitution to prosperity here, yet he expected a glorious resurrection hereafter. The parts of the chapter are four. 1. A preface to verse 4. 2. a pathetical persuasion of his friends to commiseration. From thence to ver. 23. 3. a clear testimony of the resurrection. From vers. 23. to vers. 28. 4. a persuasion of his friends to repentance. From thence to the end of the chapter. In the preface note first the speaker set out verse 1; 2. a complaint of the harshness of his friends against him set out, 1. by the continuance of their vexations, vers. 2. 2. by the frequency of their calumniations, verse 3.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4. 1. & 8. 1.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{How long will ye vex my soul, and break me in pieces with words?}* Will you never make an end of your cruel speeches, whereby you fill me with grief who am too full of it already? Is this to comfort the afflicted, to restore the broken-hearted, to lift up him that is cast down? He pays Bildad in his own coin, chap. 18. 2. You demand of me, when I will make an end of answering, but I say to you again, when will you leave off thus miserably to vex me, and with these your reproachful speeches to kill my heart?

*{How long}* An interrogation with indignation. See the like, chap. 8. 2. & 18. 1.

*{will ye vex}* Put me to trouble and grief, and labor to defend myself. So much the word imports. He speaks in the plural number, because the rest had vexed him, as well as Bildad.

*{my soul}* See on chap. 14. 22. The person may be meant here, *How long will ye vex me?* But with relation to the soul, as if he had said, You see what a case my body is in, full of sores. God has spared my soul. But you add soul-afflictions to body-afflictions, and grieve my very soul in seeking to make me a wicked man, and putting me out of God's protection; see the like Psal. 3. 2. We say in our English expression, when we would set out a great injury, He vexed me to the very soul.

*{and break me in pieces}* Or, *crush me*. As the word is translated, Job 4. 19. you diminish the strength and comfort of my soul.

*{with words}* With your bitter and invective speeches! Job could bear many losses, and among the rest the loss of his dearest children, chap. 1. 21, 22. but cannot bear bad language of so high a strain, as to question his integrity.

**V. 3. ***{These ten times have ye reproached me: you are not ashamed that you make yourselves strange to me.}* The thing objected against them in this verse is, their often shameless reproaching of him.

*{These ten times}* That is, oftentimes, A certain number for an uncertain, as Gen. 31. 7. Numb. 14. 22. Levit. 26. 26. Nehem. 4. 12. Eccles. 7. 19. Zech. 8. 23. Though they had yet made but five speeches against him, yet in every one of those they had often reproached him. If but one of you had but accused and reproached an innocent man, it had been an inhuman carriage. What is it then for you to reproach me often, and to continue still in your calumnies against me?

*{have ye reproached me}* You have disgraced me, and spoken contumeliously of me, Ruth 2. 15. Judg. 18. 7.

*{you are not ashamed}* Yet you do not so much as blush, nor are not a whit ashamed of all the wrong you have done me. Others read it by way of interrogation, and that is more emphatical. *Are ye not ashamed, &c.* You should be so, if you had any shame in you.

*{that you make yourselves strange to me}* That you deal harshly with me, as with a stranger and foreigner, and not lovingly, as with a friend. Or, *that you harden your selves against me*. That you deal impudently with me.

**V. 4. ***{And be it indeed, that I have erred, mine error remaineth with myself}* Job does not acknowledge, that he was punished for any wickedness, but if it were so, yet his friends should not deal so harshly with him, nor add to his afflictions, but let him quietly bear the punishment, which God had laid upon him. Thus he comes to the second part of the chapter to persuade his friends to commiseration of his sad condition. First, he shows vers. 4, 5. that they deal unequally with him, though he were worse than he is. Secondly, he lays down at large, what heavy pressures God had laid upon him, from vers. 6. to vers. 13. 3. How unkindly all his friends had dealt with him, from thence to vers. 20. 4. How low he was brought, vers. 20. 5. He entreats his friends to pity him, whom God so sorely follows with his judgments.

*{And be it indeed}* Heb. *and also surely*. Suppose I had erred, which yet I do not confess.

*{that I have erred}* That I had committed some great wicked act out of error or infirmity. Or, that I had erred in my disputations with you, and been too stiff in maintaining mine own innocency.

*{mine error remaineth with myself}* Heb. *Lodgeth, or tarrieth all night*. Let my error be what it will, I must suffer for it, and not you, and therefore you need not be so harsh against me. Or, it may be I have erred, yet you have not convinced me of error. And therefore I must still be of the same mind, till I see my error.

**V. 5. ***{If indeed ye will magnify yourselves against me, and plead against me my reproach}* Though I had erred, yet it did not become you to insult over me for my error.

*{If indeed}* If you be resolved to triumph thus over me, remember that God has laid these afflictions on me, verse 6. and punished me sorely for my error. It may be that thought may make you alter your resolution, and deal more kindly with me. But the word אם oftentimes notes out a question, and then it may be translated thus, *Will ye indeed magnifie your selves against me?* And so it may be knit to the former verse, and the sense be this, Suppose I have erred, and persist in mine error, as being not yet convicted of it, will you be so severe against me, as to triumph perpetually over me? This particle is translated interrogatively, chap. 17. 2. see there.

*{ye will magnify yourselves}* You will set up yourselves in your words, and pull down me, commend yourselves, and discommend me. Set up your own tongues, and speak big words against me. Psal. 55. 12. & 38. 16. Jer. 48. 26, 42. Or, *ye will magnifie it*. To wit, mine error by making it greater than it is, to disparage me.

*{against me}* Who am low already, and may easily be trampled under foot.

*{and plead against me}* Bring my wretched case against me for an argument to reproach me for a wicked man. see on chap. 15. 3. upon the word *Reason*.

*{my reproach}* My error, as you conceive, vers. 4. which you think to be a reproach to me. Or, *to my reproach*. Cast it in my teeth to my disgrace, chap. 20. 3.

**V. 6. ***{Know now that God hath overthrown me, and hath compassed me with his net}* Here Job begins to reckon up the manifold miseries, which God had inflicted on him. And does it partly in Bildad's own words. He confesses, that God had taken him in his nets, as Bildad had said, he used to do wicked men. Chap. 18. 8, 9. As if he had said, I deny not that which you say, and I would have you more and more to observe it, that I am overthrown by God's own hand: but I would not have you to add affliction to those whom God has afflicted, but to pity me the more.

*{know}* Take special notice of it.

*{now}* Or, *then*. See on chap. 17. 15.

*{that God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{hath overthrown me}* Chap. 32. 13. Has overthrown my estate, and deprived me of all means. Or, has overthrown my righteous cause in not suffering me to plead for myself. The word properly signifies to bow down or make one crooked, Psal. 38. 6. see on chap. 8. 3.

*{and hath compassed me with his net}* Heb. *and hath compassed his net about me*. Or, *and his net hath compassed me about*. He has brought me into such troubles, that I am like a bird or wild beast in a net, taken and tied so fast, that I cannot possibly get out. A similitude taken from fowlers and hunters. chap. 10. 16. & 18. 8. Lam. 1. 13. Ezek. 12. 13. Hos. 7. 12.

**V. 7. ***{Behold I cry out of wrong, but I am not heard: I cry aloud, but there is no judgment.}* How miserable a condition he was in being thus taken in God's nets he further shows, because as he could not help himself, so he cried in vain to others for relief, seeing neither God nor man would hearken to him to deliver him.

*{Behold}* It is a remarkable thing, that I being thus unjustly afflicted should cry for help and find none. See on chap. 1. 12. Or, *If I cry out of wrong, I am not heard*. So הן may be translated, chap. 9. 12, see notes there.

*{I cry out}* This shows the vehemency of his afflictions, who in his miseries did not call faintly upon God, but cried strongly for help and deliverance.

*{of wrong}* Or, *of violence*. That I am without cause thus heavily afflicted by God, and derided by men. See the like complaints, Jer. 20. 8. Hab. 1. 2.

*{but I am not heard}* For this word, see on chap. 16. 1. on the word *Answered*. So Job means here, I am not answered. God pities me no more than a deaf man that cannot hear. He will not deliver me, Lam. 3. 8. Psal. 22. 2.

*{I cry aloud}* As men ready to perish cry aloud for present help.

*{but there is no judgment}* See on chap. 8. 3. & 9. 32. It is a just thing that I should be delivered, yet I cannot obtain it, chap. 34. 5.

**V. 8. ***{He hath fenced up my way, that I cannot pass, and he hath set darkness in my paths}* Job having now mentioned his sorrows falls upon a large repetition and enumeration of them being carried away as it were with a torrent of grief. And turns away his speech from his friends to God, without naming him, as he had done, chap. 16. 7. he sets out his remediless condition, as he conceives it, by a similitude taken from a traveler. Two things are most troublesome in a journey, hedges, and darkness. The latter hinders him from the sight of his way: the former from walking in it. So had God dealt with him, that what way soever he went, he could not get out of his miseries.

*{He}* God mentioned vers. 6. and cried unto for help, vers. 6.

*{hath fenced up}* See on chap. 3. 23. see the like, Lam. 3. 7, 9. Hos. 2. 6. he has cast obstacles in my way.

*{my way}* My life which is so full of troubles. A metaphor from travelers.

*{that I cannot pass}* I can find no way, that can lead me out of my afflictions. For the word, see on chap. 15. 19.

*{and he hath set darkness in my paths}* He has laid so great troubles on me, that all comfort is gone, and I can see no means of escaping. For the word Darkness, see on chap. 15. 22, 23. for the word Path, see on Job 18. 10. upon the word, *Way*.

**V. 9. ***{He hath stripped me of my glory, and taken the crown from my head}* The second comparison to set out the greatness of his misery is taken from the habit of the body. As it were a heavy thing, if a man gorgeously appareled, and having a stately crown on his head should in an instant be deprived of all this: so is my condition, God has stripped me of all those things, which made me excel others, and honorable in their eyes.

*{He hath stripped me}* He has taken all my glory away, as a beast has all his skin flayed off by the butcher, Lev. 1. 6. Mic. 3. 3. or as a man robbed, that has all his clothes taken off, and is left naked, Lev. 6. 11. Gen. 37. 23.

*{of my glory}* Heb. *my glory from upon me*. That is, my riches, authority, dignity, glorious apparel, children, and all for which men respected me above others, Job 29. 7, 14, 20. & 30. 11. Hos. 9. 11. Gen. 31. 1. Psal. 49. 16, 17. Isa. 61. 6. & 66. 11. Garments are used to set out glory, Exod. 28. 2. Mat. 6. 29. And the putting off of stately apparel is used for a note of disgrace, 2 Sam. 6. 20.

*{and taken}* Heb. *and removed*. See on Chap. 12. 20.

*{the crown}* That which was my greatest ornament, as a crown is a king's. He means the same things which he intended by glory before in this verse. Lam. 5. 16. Prov. 17. 6. & 14. 24. Psal. 65. 11. the word signifies such a crown as compasses a man's head about, and does as it were separate him from other men, 1 Sam. 23. 26. Psal. 8. 5. & 103. 4. & 142. 7.

*{from mine head}* Heb. *of my head*. Which did adorn my head. Kings use to wear their crowns on their heads, as the most honorable member, and that they may be most conspicuous. The word ראש signifies. 1. The head, which is the beginning or highest part of the body, Prov. 1. 9. 2. The highest part of anything, which stands above the rest, as the head above the other members, Gen. 28. 12. 3. A commander in war or peace, who is above the rest, and guides them by reason, as the head does the members, Numb. 14. 4. 4. The beginning of a thing, Judg. 7. 19. 5. The chief part of anything, or most excellent, as the head is in the body, Exod. 30. 23. 6. The sum of things, which is the head of number, Exod. 30. 12. Here it is taken in the first sense for the head of the body.

**V. 10. ***{He hath destroyed me on every side, and I am gone: and mine hope hath he removed like a tree.}* The third comparison is taken from pulling houses down, and rooting up trees in this verse, of which there is no hope of building up, or growing any more. No not so much as is granted, Chap. 14. 8, 9. This Bildad foretold should be the end of wicked men, Chap. 18. 6. &c. And this Job confesses to have befallen him, though he do not confess himself to be a wicked man.

*{He hath destroyed me}* He has pulled me down, as a man pulls down a house. So much the word imports, Lev. 14. 45. Judg. 8. 17. And it answers fitly to Job's losses, which came one presently after another like the pieces of a house tumbling down. See Chap. 1.

*{on every side}* Heb. *round about*. As Chap. 18. 11. see there; as also on Chap. 16. 13. & 18. 11. As an enemy compasses a rich city about and enters on all parts, and spoils all, so God had spoiled Job of all his goods, and as it were wounded him in all parts of his body.

*{and I am gone}* He has stripped me of all and I am going out of the world, see on Chap. 14. 20. you may count me as a dead man, I am as good as gone already.

*{and mine hope}* To wit, of restoration to corporal health and prosperity, Chap. 17. 15. not of eternal life, for that remained firm in him, vers. 25. & 13. 15 & 14. 14.

*{hath he removed}* Heb. *made to go away*. Hence the removes of the children of Israel had their name, Numb. 33. 1, 2, 3.

*{like a tree}* I have no more hope to recover my health and prosperity, than a tree plucked up by the roots. This Bildad threatened also to the wicked man, Chap. 18. 16. For the word see on Chap. 14. 7.

**V. 11. ***{He hath also kindled his wrath against me, and he counteth me unto him, as one of his enemies.}* The fourth comparison is taken from the wars in this verse and the next. Herein Job compares God to a powerful king, who being exceeding angry, prepares himself to be revenged of his adversaries, and in his anger besieges them, and puts them to great straits.

*{He also hath kindled}* Anger is compared to fire, which being blown up, rises to a great flame, and does much hurt.

*{his wrath}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5, 13. & 14. 13.

*{against me}* He has singled me out to exercise his wrath upon me, Chap. 7. 20.

*{and he counteth me unto him}* He deals so sharply with me, as if he accounted me an utter enemy, and had a full purpose to destroy me. Thus he uses to deal with his foes, see on Chap. 13. 24.

*{as one of his enemies}* Heb. *as his enemies*. He makes no more account of me than he does of his enemies: so the plural is put for the singular, Judg. 12. 7. Zech. 9. 9. Psal. 1. 1. See on Job 13. 24. & 16. 9. This is objected to Job by Elihu, Chap. 33. 10. He afflicts me as men use to afflict their enemies.

**V. 12. ***{His troops come together, and raise up their way against me, and encamp round about my tabernacle.}* He goes on in soldiers' terms, who when they purpose to besiege a city, first call all their troops together to go against it, then cast up a trench, and last of all begirt it round. So did God begirt him with afflictions. Because he had mentioned God's dealing with him as an enemy, now he makes mention of an army prepared against him. All the engines of the enemy are set on work at once against me, and have stripped me of all my goods.

*{His troops}* Abundance of afflictions like enemies threatening to ruin me. See on Chap. 16. 13. Troubles are God's soldiers, and sicknesses fight under his banner, and he sends them sometimes by troops, Matth. 8. 8, 9.

*{came}* See on Chap. 15. 21.

*{together}* My miseries came like troops of enemies, and set upon me all at once to undo me, Chap. 1. and my friends to undo my good name, vers. 2, 3. of this chapter.

*{and raise up}* As soldiers use to do, to raise the ground either to pass through dirty places upon causeways, or to make trenches and fortifications against besieged cities.

*{their way against me}* See on Chap. 17. 9.

*{and encamp round about}* Are as it were set down on every side of me, as soldiers about a besieged city. I cannot look out of doors, but more troubles seize upon me.

*{my tabernacle}* My dwelling. See on Chap. 5. 24.

**V. 13. ***{He hath put my brethren far from me, and mine acquaintance are verily estranged from me.}* What, how great, and how many Job's troubles were, he had showed by choice metaphors, now he shows in plain terms how much discommodity, loss, shame, and contempt, attended his sharp trials. Men in affliction look for comfort from their friends and kindred, but his friends forsook him, which often befalls God's children in their afflictions, and is a great addition to their sorrows. He shows, 1. How he was forsaken by his friends and kindred, verse 13, 14. 2. By those of his household, verse 15, 16, 17. 3. By his intimate and most familiar friends, verse 19. 4. How low he was brought in his own body, vers. 20.

*{He hath put far from me}* Heb. *from about me*. He attributes this strangeness of friends to God. He had put them all out of his sight, when he had most need of their presence and comfort. Some were indeed with him, but better for him they had been absent, for they were miserable comforters, Chap. 16. 2.

*{my brethren}* The word אח signifies. 1. A natural brother born of the same parents, Chap. 1. 18. 2. A kinsman, Gen. 13. 8. & 14. 14. 3. One of the same country, Gen. 29. 7. 4. One of the same office, 1 King. 20. 32, 33. Here it may be taken in the first sense, for natural brethren, because his kinsfolk are mentioned distinctly, ver. 14. And he might have brethren left, as well as kindred, though he had no children. See another sense on Chap. 30. 29.

*{and mine acquaintance}* Who would acknowledge me before, and take some care of me, and show some respect of me. See the like complaint, Psal. 31. 11. & 38. 11. & 69. 8. & 88. 8, 18.

*{are verily estranged from me}* They take no more care of me than if they had never known me. Some read it, *Surely estranged*. It is too true, I find it to my sorrow. Others, *Onely estranged*. They are only as strangers to leave me, not as friends to help me in my misery. Others, *Utterly estranged*. That is, they have clean forsaken me.

**V. 14. ***{My kinsfolk have failed, and my familiar friends have forgotten me.}* So great was my misery, that not only my kindred forsook me, but the most familiar friends I had in the world, which use to stick closer to us than our brethren, Prov. 18. 24.

*{My kinsfolk}* The word קרוב first signifies near, as Exod. 12. 4. 2. A neighbor that dwells by a man, Exod. 32. 27. 3. A kinsman who is near in blood, Ruth 2. 20. Here it is put for kindred.

*{have failed}* Heb. *have ceased*; to wit, to visit or do any friendly office to me. We were joined together in blood, but my calamity has divided us.

*{and my familiar friends}* Broughton translates it elegantly, *They whom I favored*. I took notice of them in their misery and did them good; they forsake me in mine: an ill requital. So the word is used for favoring, Psal. 1. 6.

*{have forgotten me}* Take no more care of me, than if they had forgotten that ever there was such a man as Job in the world.

**V. 15. ***{They that dwell in mine house, and my maids count me for a stranger: I am an alien in their sight}* Job having showed what ill measure he had received abroad, now sets down, what coarse entertainment he had at home.

*{They that dwell in my house}* Heb. *They that sojourn*. Some understand it of widows, and orphans, whom Job kept in his house: but it may better be understood of hirelings, which were as strangers abiding in the family but for a time, whereas bought servants or slaves continued there all their days unless their masters set them at liberty, Exod. 21. 4, 6. Now that Job had such hirelings in his house may well be conjectured by the multitude of employments in so great an estate, and by his similitude taken from thence, Chap. 7. 1, 2. For the word House, see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{and my maids}* They which daily tended on me in my health and prosperity, now neglect me in my sickness and adversity.

*{count me}* See on Chap. 18. 3. They neglect me and look no more after me, than they would do after a mere stranger, to whom they never had any relation.

*{for a stranger}* See on verse 13.

*{I am an alien}* That is, I am as a stranger, no more regarded, than a traveler in a foreign country. So the note of likeness is omitted, Chap. 7. 7.

*{in their sight}* See on Chap. 15. 15.

**V. 16. ***{I called my servant, and he gave me no answer: I intreated him with my mouth.}* He sets out the impudence of his lasting servants, which should have been more kind than hirelings, and shows how much they contemned their master, in that when their master was in sore and great misery, and entreated their help, they denied it to him, which no man would have done to a stranger in such a condition, but he that had no humanity left him in.

*{I called}* I called him to come to me to help me in my misery. For the word see on Chap. 13. 22.

*{my servant}* Him that should have attended on me without calling, and would have done it in my prosperity.

*{and he gave me no answer}* He was far from coming to me, or helping me, that would not vouchsafe his master an answer. For the word see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{I intreated him}* I forgot that I was a master, or an equal. I came so low that I entreated him, as if he had been my superior: yet he would neither hear nor help me. It may be read, *Though I intreated him*, as verse 17. 27. He has forgotten that he is a servant, and is far from obeying my commands, that will not hear my entreaties. Hereby it appears, that Job had his house still and had servants in it to live upon what was left; though they would do no duty to him.

*{with my mouth}* With fair words. See on Chap. 15. 5. I did not send to them by others, but spoke to them myself, yet they would not hear.

**V. 17. ***{My breath is strange to my wife, though I intreated for the children's sake of mine own body.}* He goes on to set out in few words the inhumanity of his wife, who neglecting the band of marriage, and her husband's prayers, could not endure her husband's breath, but left him helpless in his misery. My very wife does loathe my breath, and cannot abide it, as being strange and unpleasant, yea even when I beseech her by those common pledges, our children, that God has given us.

*{My breath}* See on Chap. 15. 2. upon vain knowledge, or knowledge of the wind. It has many significations, but here it is taken for the breath, as also Eccles. 3. 19.

*{is strange}* See on verse 13.

*{to my wife}* My sores and ulcers do so stink, that they make my very breath unsavory, which makes my wife neglect me, and keep far off from me, as if I were unknown to her. It is likely that he had inward corruption, as well as outward, which made his breath to stink.

*{though I intreated}* Though I did not charge her as a husband, but entreat her as an inferior. See on verse 16.

*{for the children's sake}* For the love that she bore to our children, and should for their sake bear to me, who had as great an interest in them as herself. This should have been a powerful argument, but could not prevail with Job's wife. Others read it, *And I intreated the children*. Thinking that Job had some lesser children left, or nephews that were not at the feast. But what help could these little ones bring him? neither are any such mentioned in the end of the book. Some understand it of Job's children by some concubines, but neither does it appear, that Job had any such, neither was it an argument to move Job's wife to pity, but rather to anger or jealousy. For the word children, see on Chap. 14. 21.

*{of mine own body}* Heb. *of my belly*. See on Chap. 15. 2. Children lie in the mother's belly, yet are said to come from the father's belly also, Psal. 132. 11. where it is also translated body, as it is here. The philosophers give a natural reason of it, because the seed whereof the child is made comes first out of the father's reins or kidneys.

**V. 18. ***{Yea young children despised me, I arose, and they spake against me.}* Now he shows with grief of heart, that he is contemned of those who honored him before, to wit, of young ones; Chap. 30. 1. and this was a great addition to his misery.

*{Yea}* See on Chap. 18. 5.

*{young children}* Such as by reason of their tender years should reverence my age, yet they seeing how much I am contemned by others, despise me also. See the like, 2 King. 2. 23. so this word is used, Chap. 21. 11. Or, *ungodly men*, as it is translated, Chap. 16. 11.

*{despised me}* They abhorred me as a thing to be loathed, and would not come near me.

*{I arose}* To rebuke them for their ill carriage toward me, or to pacify them, or to show respect to them, or to go away from them. Or, as soon as I am up in the morning. Or, *I stood still, yet they went on to reproach me*.

*{and they spake against me}* Others read the end of the verse thus, *when I arose then they spake against me*. They use reproaching and reviling words against me.

**V. 19. ***{All my inward friends abhorred me: and they whom I loved, are turned against me}* Now follows that which was hardest of all to be brooked, and went nearest to Job, to wit, that he was an abomination to his most intimate friends, and found them adversaries, to whom he had showed most love and affection. As if he had said, all those with whom before time I communicated my secrets, as with my dearest friends, do now turn their face from me, neither do any deal more despitefully with me, than those of whom I have best deserved, and whom I have most trusted.

*{All}* Not some alone, but every one. I have not one friend left, in whom I may confide.

*{my inward friends}* Heb. *mortals of my counsel*. Those men that are mortal like myself, and therefore should pity me in my misery, and to whom I communicated my secret and weightiest affairs in my prosperity, as men do to their best friends, lest they should be revealed. These had great advantage against him, and might do him much harm. Of such David complains, Psal. 41. 9. & 55. 13, 14. For the word, Counsel, see on Chap. 15. 8. upon the word, *Secret*.

*{abhorred me}* See on Chap. 15. 16. They would not come near me, but kept aloof off from me. The word תעב signifies that which is displeasing to all the senses, which a man would neither see, hear, smell, taste, nor touch, so much he does detest it. Hateful to the sight, from which a man turns his eyes away, Exod. 8. 26. To the hearing, so that men turn their ears away, Am. 5. 10. To the taste, against which a man shuts his mouth, Psal. 107. 18. To the smell, so that the nose is stopped against it, Isa. 1. 13. To the touching, so that the finger will not touch it, Lev. 11. 13, 24. Hereby Job shows that his best friends did utterly detest him, and forsake him in his miseries, which was a very great addition to his sorrows.

*{and they whom I loved}* Heb. *and this man whom I loved*. The singular for the plural, as if he had pointed to his friends, and said these who were most of my counsel and best beloved by me, are now every one of them become mine enemies. Or, it may be he strikes at Bildad in particular, that spoke last to him. All my friends abhor me, yea even this Bildad, whom I highly prized, that now speaks so bitterly against me, he joins with the rest to condemn me unjustly. This is well joined to the former, for those whom men love, they use to make of their privy counsel.

*{are turned against me}* Are become mine enemies, and set themselves against me to do me hurt. It was a great grief to him for such friends to fail him in his misery, but greater for them to hurt him, and to change their love into hatred.

**V. 20. ***{My bone cleaveth to my skin, and to my flesh, and I am escaped with the skin of my teeth}* Job now concludes his discourse of his harsh entertainment by others, with a description of his own low condition to move his friends the more to compassion. He was forsaken by brethren, neighbors, kindred, such as he had done good to, hirelings, maidservants, menservants, wife and all (for children he had none left) despised by children, and friends, and withal brought so low in his body, that he was become a mere anatomy having nothing left on him but skin and bones. A fit object for commiseration.

*{My bone}* That is, my bones. The singular, for the plural, as Chap. 2. 5.

*{cleaveth to my skin}* My flesh being all gone, my bones and skin now grow together. This argues an extreme leanness and consumption of his flesh. Others take the meaning to be, that his bones appeared through his skin, but that the words will hardly bear; see the like phrase, Psal. 102. 3. Lam. 4. 8.

*{and to my flesh}* Or, *as to my flesh*. As it was wont to cleave to my flesh, when I was well, so now that being consumed, it cleaves to my skin. See on Chap. 18. 20. and for the word, Flesh, on Chap. 14. 22.

*{and I am escaped}* I am yet alive, but have scarce any whole skin left, but what is on my teeth. The rest is all full of sores. Some conceive that the devil left his mouth whole, that he might have liberty to curse God, whereunto Satan aimed to bring him as before, by his losses, Chap. 1. 11. so now by his sores, Chap. 2. 5.

*{with the skin of my teeth}* Some understand it of his lips, which cover the teeth, as the skin does the body. But it is likely his lips had sores as well as other parts, Chap. 2. 7. and the Scripture uses not so to speak of the lips. It is rather meant therefore of the skin that covers the gums, the flesh being worn away there also, and nothing but skin left on the roots of his teeth, and no sores here within, as were all over his body without. For the word, Teeth, see on Chap. 16. 9.

**V. 21. ***{Have pity upon me, have pity upon me, O ye my friends; for the hand of God hath touched me}* Here Job goes on from a lamentable description of his troubles to a pathetical insinuation to his friends that he expects, that they having weighed all the particulars, should rather pity him, than so furiously prosecute him. This he presses with a double argument, one in this verse from those great plagues, which God had laid upon him, which might suffice, and need not be aggravated by their unkindness: the other from his low condition in the next verse who had no flesh left, and therefore had little need to be further persecuted. All that he had spoken before was to move them to compassion, and now he moves them directly to it. You shall show yourselves most hardhearted creatures, if so lamentable a spectacle of one so afflicted and forsaken of his nearest friends will not move you to pity, which might work upon a heart of flint.

*{Have pity upon me}* Forbear to reproach me in this manner, and speak comfortably to me in my afflictions.

*{have pity upon me}* The doubling of the word shows that Job had great need, and earnest desire of commiseration. Others take it for a diminution. If you will not relieve or help me in my misery, at least pity me. This is the least courtesy a man can look for from his friends.

*{O ye my friends}* Speaking so particularly to them shows his great desire of comfort from them. You that once carried yourselves as friends to me in my prosperity, now show yourselves not like those false ones lately spoken of, but pity me in my adversity. If others neglect me, you should not do so. For the word, Friends, see on Chap. 16. 20.

*{for the hand}* An argument from God's afflicting him to move men to pity him. Else they might sin against God, Psal. 69. 26. For the word, Hand, see on Chap. 15. 23. Here is meant God's striking hand, or his plague.

*{of God}* The powerful God, who can lay on load: see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{hath touched me}* See on Chap. 1. 11.

**V. 22. ***{Why do ye persecute me as God, and are not satisfied with my flesh?}* As the heaviness of God's hand upon him, so the weakness and low estate it brought upon his body should have moved his friends to pity him.

*{Why}* The question adds emphasis to Job's expostulation. What reason have you to be thus severe against me? What cause have I given you to use me thus harshly?

*{do ye persecute me}* Follow me so closely with reproaches to do me hurt.

*{as God}* As if you had as much power over me as God, and might lay what load you would upon me. Or, why do you join with God in persecuting me, and add scorns to my sores? You should not do so, see on vers. 21. For the word God, see on Chap. 8. vers. 3, 5.

*{and are not satisfied}* Are not filled. Think I have not hurt enough already. see on Chap. 7. 4. & 10. 15. on the word, *Full*.

*{with my flesh}* See on Chap. 14. 22. You are like wild beasts, which are not content to eat the flesh, but break the bones of their prey: So you are not content, that my flesh is consumed with sores and pain, but you add sorrows also to my mind to break it with your reproaches, Chap. 31. 31.

**V. 23. ***{Oh that my words were now written, Oh that they were printed in a book!}* Job now goes on to another part of the chapter, the confirmation of his integrity, wherein. 1. He submits himself to man's judgment, vers. 23, 24. 2. To God, vers. 25, 26, 27. Bildad seems to reckon Job among those that knew not God, Chap. 18. vers. 21. Job therefore here professes his faith in God and Christ his Redeemer, and his expectation of a joyful resurrection from him. In the first he first desires to approve himself to the judgment of the present age, vers. 23. secondly, of the age to come, vers. 24. Do not think that I babble I know not what, but seeing you have no care at all of comforting or relieving me, I wish for this one last solace, that those words which I shall now speak in your hearing, were recorded in some public instrument to all eternity.

*{Oh that}* Heb. *who will give that*! The form of a wish in Hebrew, see on Chap. 6. 8. & 11. 5.

*{my words}* Which I now shall speak concerning my integrity, and the resurrection.

*{were now written}* That some cunning scribe were present, that could take them now presently, as I speak them, and not lose one of them. For the word, Now, see on chap. 17. 15.

*{Oh that they were printed}* Or, *drawn out*. That is, written in great and capital letters, that every man might read them, Hab. 2. 2. for there was no printing in those days. He wishes they were, not written only in an ordinary way, but in a large and lasting hand, as laws and public records used to be written, Isa. 30. 8.

*{in a book}* Not in loose paper only, that may be lost, but in a book where they might be kept together, as public records are engrossed in a book, Gen. 5. 1. Matth. 1. 1. Esth. 6. 1. And Kimchi in his preface to the small prophets gives this as a reason, why they were all twelve bound together in a book, lest by reason of the smallness of them any one of them should be lost. Job would have none of his precious words to be lost. Also he wishes them in a book that they might come to many men's knowledge, that could not hear him speak them, nor perhaps might never hear of them by report.

**V. 24. ***{That they were graven with an Iron pen, and lead, in the rock for ever}* To be written in a book is more than to be written, and to be graven in a rock is more than to be written in a book. Hereby Job shows, that as he feared not the judgment of all then alive, so not of posterity neither, who use to judge more freely and impartially of things, when fear and favor of the persons is gone with them. As if he had said, I could be content my words were written in a book, but I wish rather they had a more lasting monument. Moths and age can corrupt books. I would therefore have an iron pen and a leaden book that may stand out all the injury of time, and preserve this testimony of the resurrection, to the resurrection. And this wish of Job God has granted him, for his words are written in God's book, which will last as long as rocks to the world's end. Else had we never heard of this excellent testimony of the resurrection.

*{That they were graven}* Cut into some hard matter, as stone, brass, or lead, where they might last longer than things written. So monuments are graven on tombs for remembrance of great men that are dead.

*{with an Iron pen}* It had need be a hard and sharp instrument, that shall grave in stone, or lead, or brass. And that which is so written by such an instrument on such hard matter will last longer, than what is written by pens on paper, Jer. 17. 1. The word ברזל 1. Signifies the iron itself, Deut. 8. 9. 2. An axe made of iron, 2 King. 6. 5. 3. An iron chain, Psal. 105. 18. 4. A thing hard or difficult, Deut. 28. 23, 48. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{and lead}* Some read it, *or of lead*. But leaden pens are too soft to cut letters in stones, although they may serve to write in paper, yet is that writing also neither so visible nor durable, as others. But lead here must be the matter of the writing, not the instrument. Some conceive that they used for preservation of monuments to cut the letters in stone, and melt lead and put into each letter to make them the more visible and durable. Others, that they did engrave the letters in plates of lead, as now they do in brass, and fasten them in stones, as now they do engrave stones. The Heb. word is taken from a word, that signifies dust, because lead is taken out of the dust, and has much dust in it.

*{in the rock}* The word צור signifies. 1. A rock or stone, as Psal. 81. 16. 2. A sharp stone, that will cut like a knife, Exod. 4. 25. 3. A strong castle or fort, for such were often built upon rocks, Psal. 61. 2. 4. Strength itself, because rocks are strong, and stand out all storms. Isa. 26. 4. everlasting strength. Heb. *a rock of ages*. 5. God himself, who is strength itself, Deut. 32. 18. Here it is taken in the first sense. Things written in rocks and stones are durable.

*{for ever}* That they might last longer than one generation, even as long as the world lasts.

**V. 25. ***{For I know that my redeemer liveth, and that he shall stand at the latter day upon the earth}* I have appealed to men present and to come: but if they should all prove corrupt, and take your part against me, yet I know there is a general judgment to come in which I shall be cleared. He sets out the doctrine of the resurrection largely. 1. By the excellent qualities of his Savior and Judge, that he is living, and shall abide, when others are in the dust, and shall be the Judge in the last and solemn judgment, in this verse. 2. From the effects of death, which brings a man very low, and consumes his skin, and pierces through his flesh, yet Christ will raise him up again, vers. 26. 3. From the effects of the resurrection, which will restore his own flesh to him again, and those eyes he now has, to look on Christ at the last day; though for a time he lie in the grave, as one swallowed up by death, vers. 27. The sum of this verse is, as if he had said, Lest haply you imagine, that because I have no hope of this life, that therefore I have none of the life to come neither: I tell you that I know that he by whom I shall be redeemed is immortal, who as he was before this dust, as being the creator of it, so shall he not be dissolved with it, but remain after it is dissolved, and brought to nothing. The Hebrew writers and Mercer with them conceive it not to be meant of Christ, nor of the resurrection, but either of Job's present condition, or of a certain expectation of prosperity in this world after all his troubles. Though Job might at present see God by the eyes of faith, yet that is not the sight here spoken of, but a bodily sight with the same eyes he now had, vers. 27. which Job could not then do. And for a certain hope of prosperity, it is clean contrary to all the tenor of Job's speech, Chap. 16. 22. & 17. 1, 11, 13, 14, 15, 16. He never dreamed in all his affliction of his following prosperity. It was a great wonder both to himself and others to see it. Therefore it must needs be meant of the sight of Christ in the resurrection, which was his greatest comfort in his miseries.

*{For}* Heb. *And*. Yet that particle is in Scripture translated for, and ushers in a reason of what is said before, see on Chap. 11. 4. & 17. 10. Job reasons from his assurance. I am sure it is true, and therefore being of great weight it is worthy to be written down. Others read it, *How, or That*. As setting down the matter to be written, Job's faith of the resurrection. So this particle is used, Chap. 6. 9.

*{I know}* I am sure of it upon good grounds. And the pronoun is added in the original for emphasis, however you may think me a wicked or an ignorant man, yet I know this great mystery.

*{that my redeemer}* An allusion to the ceremonial law, where the nearest kinsman was to take the wife, and buy the land, Ruth 3. 9, 12, 13. with Ruth 4. 4, 5. Hereby is meant Christ our Redeemer, who is of our kindred by incarnation, and redeems us by his passion. That Job knew this, see on Chap. 16. 20, 21. & 17. 3. This may more fitly be applied to Christ, than to God the Father (to whom the Jews apply it) for the Father never did redeem persons, nor inheritances in the law, but the brother, or nearest kinsman: and Christ is our brother by incarnation, and we are his brethren by adoption. We were Satan's by nature, Christ has redeemed us by the price of his own blood, and will deliver us from hell, and settle us in heaven the inheritance of God's children, and therefore deserves the name of a Redeemer. He calls him His Redeemer, a particle of love, and challenging a right in him, else no good would come to Job, though others were, if he were not redeemed by him. Such interest expressed by this particle My, see 1 King. 20. 32. Isa. 49. 4.

*{liveth}* That is, lives forever. You and I must die, but my comfort is, I have one to right me, who lives forever. This is true of Christ as God. It was God's common oath, Numb. 14. 28. Deut. 32. 40. And also as man after his resurrection, Heb. 7. 25. Rev. 1. 18.

*{and that he shall stand}* For the word, see on Chap. 14. 12. on the word, Riseth, for it signifies both to rise, and to stand. He believes that Christ as a conqueror shall stand, and keep the field when all his enemies shall be overcome, and shall raise his again out of the dust to eternal glory. Or, *shall remain forever*. Or, *shall appear in glory to judge the quick and the dead*. For appearing is a kind of rising, as on Chap. 24. 12.

*{at the latter day}* When he comes to judge the quick and the dead. Or, *the last*, when he has overcome all his enemies, 1 Cor. 15. 25, 26. Rev. 1. 8, 11.

*{upon the earth}* Heb. *upon the dust*. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 8. Either it is to be understood metaphorically for conquering all his enemies, as before, or else the last judgment may be on the earth. Or, *over the earth*. To wit, in the air. Others read it, *and this shall stand up at the last day upon the earth*. That is, this body of mine, see on vers. 2. and Chap. 13. 28. As Christ will appear in glory at the end of the world, so he will raise me up to meet him. Or, *He will raise me up, &c*. so the word is used actively, Chap. 41. 10. Or, *He shall stand over the dust*; that is, over my dead body turned to dust to raise it, Gen. 3. 19.

**V. 26. ***{And though after my skin worms destroy this body, yet in my flesh shall I see God}* Job now pointing to his body acknowledges, that it must come under the power of death, yet believes it should be raised up to see Christ in the flesh. As if he had said, I, whom you judge a wicked man, after that the worms have pierced through this skin, and devoured this image of a body, rather than a body, even I shall be raised out of the grave again to behold God.

*{And though after my skin}* After they have devoured my skin, and pierced through it to consume my flesh.

*{worms destroy this body}* Heb. *They destroy this*. That is, the worms, who commonly destroy men's bodies in the grave. So they embalmed Joseph. That is, such as used to embalm men, Gen. 50. 26. And he says, This, and would not name his body, because it was so poor, that it was not worth the name of a body. See on verse 25. and on Chap. 13. 28. Or, it may be read impersonally, *This be destroyed*, see on Chap. 3. 3.

*{yet in my flesh}* Heb. *out of my flesh*. For the eyes are the windows of the body, out of which men look. I shall have the same body I now have, and with it behold God. For the word Flesh, see on Chap. 14. 22. Or, *with my flesh*. That is, having this flesh again. So מן is translated, vers. 22.

*{I shall see God}* I shall see Christ, who is God, in his human nature. For the word God, see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5. The whole verse may be read thus, *And after I shall awake, though this body be destroyed, yet out of my flesh shall I see God*. It is no new thing in Scripture to compare death to sleep, and resurrection to awaking, Dan. 12. 2. Ephes. 5. 14. And being thus read it is a plainer and fuller confession of the resurrection.

**V. 27. ***{Whom I shall see for myself, and mine eyes shall behold and not another; though my reins be consumed within me}* Lest any man should think, because of the difficulty of raising up the body, or the great alteration of qualities, and great glory added to it, that it should be a new created body, and not the same, that Job now had, he affirms in very many words, that it shall be the same body restored. He expounds the end of the former verse in the beginning of this, and the beginning of it in the end of this verse. As if he had said, I myself shall see this God my Savior, and with these mine eyes, and not being transformed into anything else, shall behold him: albeit as now my state is, whatsoever is remaining of this life within me, is spent even to my very bowels.
