*{whom I shall see}* See on the end of vers. 26.

*{for myself}* For my own good, and to my singular comfort. Or, Not by a deputy, as follows in this verse. but with mine own eyes, as follows immediately.

*{and mine eyes}* A farther declaration of what was said before from the instrument of seeing, which shows it to be a bodily sight.

*{shall behold}* I shall view Christ thoroughly in all his glory, and forget all my forepast miseries. The word ראה has many significations. 1. To see or observe a thing, Gen. 45. 12. 2. Metaphorically for the acts of the other senses, as for smelling, Gen. 27. 27. for feeling, Isa. 44. 16. Jer. 17. 8. for hearing, Exod. 20. 18. 3. To know, that is, to see with the eyes of the mind, Gen. 42. 1. 4. To have experience of a thing, Eccles. 1. 16. 5. To provide, Gen. 22. 8. 6. To set out prophetical visions. Hence prophets were called Seers, Isa. 30. 10. Here it is taken in the first sense for viewing Christ with the bodily eye.

*{and not another}* Heb. *and not a stranger*. My own body shall be restored to me, and not a strange body bestowed on me, wherewith to see Christ, Isa. 26. 19. 1 Cor. 15. 53. I shall not need to take it upon report from others, as it were at the second hand, neither shall I be changed, as if I were a stranger from myself, but I who now feel these sore pains shall see that happy sight.

*{though my reins}* See on Chap. 16. 13.

*{be consumed}* Here he intimates, that his pains were so very great, that they had pierced into his very bowels, and worn them also.

*{within me}* Heb. *in my bosom*. Eccles. 7. 9. in secret, so as I feel it, though none of you can see it, and therefore pity me not. Yet I shall rise again, though I be brought thus low. Or rather, though my body shall be altogether consumed in the grave both within and without, yet I shall rise again. Others read the end of the verse thus, *My reins are consumed within me*. Leaving out Though. And they interpret it thus, I have a very great desire, and I am almost consumed with looking for that happy day, when I shall see Christ in the flesh at the last day. For the seat of desire is placed in the reins. See the like expression. Joh. 2. 17. Psal. 119. 81. 2 Sam. 13. 39. Psal. 84. 2.

**V. 28. ***{But ye should say, Why persecute we him? seeing the root of the matter is found in me.}* Here is the conclusion of this chapter wherein Job 1. Shows how his friends should have carried themselves to him in this sad condition, in this verse. 2. He threatens judgment upon them from a just, and angry God, if they persist to vex him, in the last verse. Some make these words to depend on the last verse to this purpose, seeing I am thus outwardly and inwardly consumed, you should pity me, and not go on to reproach and persecute me. And they should read it, *Therefore ye should say*. As ו is used, Psal. 116. 10. But it may depend upon all their speeches against him, and be a fit conclusion to persuade them to use him better. As if Job had said, Surely equity itself, and true friendship ought rather to have withheld you from reviling and reproaching me in this manner. For if you will diligently, and with judgment weigh, and consider the whole matter, you shall find that I have true piety in me, though you condemn me for a hypocrite. The words would be plainer, if they were inverted thus, *But seeing the root of the matter is in me, ye should say, Why do we persecute him?* So Job's own words would be clearly distinguished from those he prescribes to his friends, and the confusion of persons avoided.

*{But}* So this particle is used, Chap. 9. 18.

*{ye should say}* So the future is translated, Obad. vers. 12. For the word see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Why persecute we him}* Why do we revile a godly man so deeply afflicted by God already? see on vers. 22.

*{seeing}* He lays down a just cause why they should not persecute him, to wit, his piety. The word is ordinarily translated And. But it is often translated Seeing, as here. See Chap. 21. 22. & 28. 21.

*{the root}* The foundation, that gives sap to, and upholds all the rest. See on Chap. 14. 8.

*{of the matter}* Some understand it of true piety. Seeing I am a godly man, although I be thus extraordinarily afflicted, you should not therefore neglect the root of piety, that is in me, and censure me for my heavy afflictions which clasp me about, as ivy grows about a tree, and threaten to overgrow and kill me. Others read it thus, *of the word*. For דבר signifies the word, Joel 1. 1. and the thing spoken of, Eccles. 7. 8. God's word and promise has taken deep root in me by faith, neither have I renounced it for all my troubles. Therefore God will acquit me, and you ought not to condemn me. Or, Those words I would have written down vers. 23. concerning my joyful resurrection are written in my heart. Why should you then reject me for a wicked man? Or, I have a good cause, and maintain the right with good arguments, and the truth will be found on my side in the end (as indeed it was, Chap. 42. 7.) therefore you should not condemn me for a wicked man. See the like profession of Job, Chap. 6. 29, 30.

*{is found}* It is now in me in truth, and will be found so in the end, when the business is thoroughly discussed. For the word see on Chap. 17. 10.

*{in me}* It lies in me, as the root in the ground, though you cannot see it. I am just like a tree blasted in the branches, but sound at root.

**V. 29. ***{Be ye afraid of the sword: for wrath bringeth the punishments of the sword, that ye may know that there is a judgment.}* Job now tells his friends, that they who judge so rashly of him, had need to fear the just judgment of God upon such as add afflictions to such as are pressed down with it already. As if he had said, I fear not that destruction which you O Bildad did threaten against those that know no God, (Chap. 17. 20, 21.) Look to it, you and your fellows rather, lest God strike you with his sword. Seek to prevent this heavy judgment of God before you feel it.

*{Be ye afraid}* Heb. *Be ye afraid for your selves*. Threaten not me, but take heed to yourselves, prevent your own danger. Thus Job ends his speech, as Bildad did his, Chap. 18. 21. But speaks not in general, and obliquely, as he did, but particularly, and home to his friends.

*{of the sword}* Of destruction by the sword, or of God's sword of revenge, lest some heavy judgment from him light upon you. A particular put for the general. Heb. *From the face of the sword*. Or, *because of the sword*. It hangs over your head, take heed of it. The sword is an instrument of executing malefactors, and is used here for a violent death, which comes often by the sword. It is used by an enemy or by a judge to inflict a cruel or just death, on a foe or malefactor, Lev. 26. 6. Ezek. 38. 21. And the face of the sword in the original shows the judgment to be present or near at hand, as a man whose face is seen is nigh us. The Heb. word comes from a word, that signifies to lay waste as the sword does whole countries in war.

*{for wrath bringeth}* The wrath of God will certainly bring destruction upon you, if you persist in your evil way, 2 Chron. 28. 13. Or, your wrath against me will call for vengeance to be poured down on your own heads. The word comes from heat in the original, and may well set out a fierce wrath.

*{the punishments of the sword}* Capital dangers, the punishments of sin. See on Chap. 7. 21. Others read it, *For wrath is among the iniquities of the sword*. That is, such fierce anger, as you have showed against me is one of those great sins, that God uses to punish by a violent death, or that deserve such a death. This is often the end of mockers and persecutors. Therefore fear it. Hereby Job shows much love to his friends, that was so careful to prevent their ruin, that so much furthered his.

*{that ye may know}* That you may take notice of it, and acknowledge it before you feel it, and so save yourselves from it, Matth. 3. 7. Or, That you may acknowledge it, when you feel it.

*{that there is a judgment}* That this world is not governed by chance, but by God's just providence, Psal. 58. 11. Or, That God the just Judge will certainly and severely punish scoffers, mockers, and rash censurers, Matth. 7. 1, 2. No doubt Job's friends knew this well enough, but wise men are oftentimes so blinded with passion that they foresee not the evil event of their foolish counsels, till it be too late. Job therefore like a faithful friend shows, that he is much moved with their danger in the midst of his miseries, and foretells them of it, that being forewarned, they might prevent it.
